Objectivity in continuum mechanics, an introduction
Motions, Eulerian and Lagrangian variables and functions, deformation gradient,

Abstract




In classical mechanics, there are two objectivities: 1- The covariant objectivity concerns the universal laws of physics required to be observer independent (true in any reference frame);
This is a main topic in this manuscript.
2- The isometric objectivity concerns the constitutive laws of materials once expressed in a reference frame.

Covariant objectivity in continuum mechanics follows Maxwell’s requirements, cf.
[
15
]
page 1:
“2. (…) The formula at which we arrive must be such that a person of any nation, by substituting for the different symbols the numerical value of the quantities as measured by his own national units, would arrive at a true result. (…) 10. (…) The introduction of coordinate axes into geometry by Des Cartes was one of the greatest steps in mathematical progress, for it reduced the methods of geometry to calculations performed on numerical quantities. The position of a point is made to depend on the length of three lines which are always drawn in determinate directions (…)
But for many purposes in physical reasoning, as distinguished from calculation, it is desirable to avoid explicitly introducing the Cartesian coordinates, and to fix the mind at once on a point of space instead of its three coordinates, and on the magnitude and direction of a force instead of its three components. This mode of contemplating geometrical and physical quantities is more primitive and more natural than the other,…”

And see the (short) historical note given in the introduction of Abraham and Marsden book “Foundations of Mechanics”
[
3
]
, about qualitative versus quantitative theory:
“Mechanics begins with a long tradition of qualitative investigation
culminating with
Kepler
and
Galileo
. Following this is the period of
quantitative theory (1687-1889) characterized by concomitant developments
in mechanics, mathematics, and the philosophy of science that are epitomized
by the works of
Newton
,
Euler
,
Lagrange
,
Laplace
,
Hamilton
, and
Jacobi
.
(…) For celestial mechanics (…) resolution we owe to the
genius of
Poincaré
, who resurrected the qualitative point of view (…) One advantage (…) is that by
suppressing unnecessary coordinates the full generality of the theory becomes
evident.”

After having defined motions, Eulerian and Lagrangian variables and functions,
we give the definition of the deformation gradient as a function.
We then obtain a simple understanding of the Lie derivatives of vector fields which meet the needs of engineers.
Then we get the velocity addition formula and verify that the Lie derivatives are objective.
Note that Cauchy would certainly have used the Lie derivatives if they had existed during his lifetime: To get a stress, Cauchy had to compare two vectors, whereas one vector is enough when using the derivatives of Lie.

We systematically start with qualitive definitions (observer independent), before quantifying with bases and/or Euclidean dot products (observer dependent).
A fairly long appendix tries to give in one manuscript the definitions, properties and interpretations, usually scattered across several books (and not always that easy to find).

A quantity
f
being given then:
g
defined by <<
g
equals
f
>> is noted
g:=f
.

Part I
Motions, Eulerian and Lagrangian descriptions, flows

1
Motions

The framework is classical mechanics, time being decoupled from space.

{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
is the classical geometric affine space (the space we live in), and

({\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}},+,.)=\{\vec{pq}:p,q\in{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}
is the associated vector space of bipoint vectors equipped with its usual rules.
We also consider
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{R}^{2}
as subspaces of
{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
, i.e. we consider
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
and
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
,
n=1,2,3
.

1.1
Referential

Origin:

An observer chooses an origin
{\cal O}\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
; Thus a point
p\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
can be located by the observer thanks to the bipoint vector
\overrightarrow{{\cal O}p}={\vec{x}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
; Hence
p={\cal O}+{\vec{x}}
, and
{\vec{x}}=\overrightarrow{{\cal O}p}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}p-{\cal O}
.

Another observer chooses an origin
\widetilde{\cal O}\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
; Thus the point
p
can also be located by this observer with the bipoint vector
\overrightarrow{\widetilde{\cal O}p}=\widetilde{\vec{x}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
; So
p={\cal O}+{\vec{x}}=\widetilde{\cal O}+\widetilde{\vec{x}}
, and
\widetilde{\vec{x}}=\overrightarrow{{\cal O}\widetilde{\cal O}}+{\vec{x}}
.

Cartesian coordinate system:

A Cartesian coordinate system in the affine space
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}

is a set
{\cal R}_{\!\rm Cart}=({\cal O},({\vec{e}}_{i})_{i=1,...,n})
,
where
{\cal O}
is an origin and
({\vec{e}}_{i}):=({\vec{e}}_{i})_{i=1,...,n}
is a basis in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
chosen by the observer.
Thus the location of a point
p\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
can quantified by the observer

\exists{\vec{x}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
s.t.

p={\cal O}+{\vec{x}}\quad\hbox{with}\quad{\vec{x}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad[\overrightarrow{{\cal O}p}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{x_{1}\cr\vdots\cr x_{n}},

(1.1)

[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[\overrightarrow{{\cal O}p}]_{|{\vec{e}}}
being the column matrix containing the components
x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
of
\overrightarrow{{\cal O}p}={\vec{x}}
in the basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
.
Another observer with his origin
O_{b}
and his Cartesian basis
({\vec{b}}_{i})_{i=1,...,n}
make the Cartesian coordinate system
{\cal R}_{\!\rm Cart,b}=({\cal O}_{b},({\vec{b}}_{i})_{i=1,...,n})
, and gets for the same position
p
in
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
,

p={\cal O}_{b}+{\vec{y}}\quad\hbox{with}\quad{\vec{y}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\widetilde{y}_{i}\widetilde{\vec{b}}_{i},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad[\overrightarrow{{\cal O}_{b}p}]_{|{\vec{b}}}=[{\vec{y}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}=\pmatrix{y_{1}\cr\vdots\cr y_{n}},

(1.2)

[{\vec{y}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}=[\overrightarrow{{\cal O}_{b}p}]_{|{\vec{b}}}
being the column matrix containing the components
y_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
of
\overrightarrow{{\cal O}_{b}p}={\vec{y}}
in the basis
({\vec{b}}_{i})
.
And
\overrightarrow{{\cal O}_{b}p}=\overrightarrow{{\cal O}_{b}{\cal O}}+\overrightarrow{{\cal O}p}
, i.e.
{\vec{y}}=\overrightarrow{\widetilde{\cal O}{\cal O}}+{\vec{x}}
, gives the relation
between
{\vec{x}}
and
{\vec{y}}
(drawing).

Chronology:

A chronology (or temporal coordinate system) is a set
{\cal R}_{\!\rm time}=(t_{0},(\Delta t))
chosen by an observer,
where
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
is the time origin,
and
(\Delta t)
is the time unit (a basis in
\vec{\mathbb{R}}
).

Referentiel:

A referential
{\cal R}
is the set

{\cal R}=({\cal R}_{\!\rm time},{\cal R}_{\!\rm Cart})=\hbox{$(t_{0},(\Delta t),{\cal O},({\vec{e}}_{i})_{i=1,...,n})$ = (``chronologie'',``Cartesian coordinate system'')},

(1.3)

made of a chronology and a Cartesian coordinate system, chosen by an observer.

In the following, to simplify the writings, the same implicit chronology is used by all observers, and a referential
{\cal R}=({\cal R}_{\!\rm time},{\cal R}_{\!\rm Cart})
will simply be noted as the reference frame
{\cal R}=({\cal O},({\vec{e}}_{i}))
(so
:={\cal R}_{\!\rm Cart}
).

1.2
Einstein’s convention (duality notation)

Starting point: The classical notation
x_{i}
for the components of a vector
{\vec{x}}
relative to a basis, cf. (
1.1
).
Then the duality notion is introduced:
x_{i}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}x^{i}
(enables to see the difference between a vector and
a function when using components).
So

{\vec{x}}=\underbrace{{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}}_{\hbox{\footnotesize classic not.}}=\underbrace{{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}}_{\hbox{\footnotesize duality not.}},\quad\hbox{and}\quad[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}\,\mathop{=}^{{\hbox{\footnotesize clas{.}}}}\pmatrix{x_{1}\cr\vdots\cr x_{n}}\mathop{=}^{{\hbox{\footnotesize dual}}}\pmatrix{x^{1}\cr\vdots\cr x^{n}}.

(1.4)

The duality notation is part of the Einstein’s convention; Moreover Einstein’s convention uses the notation
{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}x^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
, i.e. the sum sign
{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}
can be omitted when an index (
i
here) is used twice, once up and once down, details at §
A.4
. However this omission of the sum sign
\sum
will not be made in this manuscript (to avoid ambiguities): The
T
e
X
-
L
a
T
e
X
program makes it easy to print
{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}
.

Example 1.1

The height of a child is represented on a wall by a vertical bipoint vector
{\vec{x}}
starting from the ground up to a pencil line.
Question: What is the size of the child ?

Answer: It depends… on the observer (quantitative value = subjective result). E.g., an English observer chooses a vertical basis vector
{\vec{a}}_{1}
which length is one English foot (ft).
So he writes
{\vec{x}}=x_{1}{\vec{a}}_{1}
,
and for him the size of the child (size of
{\vec{x}}
) is
x_{1}
in foot. E.g.
x_{1}=4
means the child is 4 ft tall.
A French observer chooses a vertical basis vector
{\vec{b}}_{1}
which length is one metre (m).
So he writes
{\vec{x}}=y_{1}{\vec{b}}_{1}
,
and for him the size of the child (size of
{\vec{x}}
) is
y^{1}
metre.
E.g., if
x_{1}=4
then
y_{1}\simeq 1.22
, since 1 ft
:=
0.3048
m
:
The child is both
4
and
1.22
tall… in foot or metre.
This quantification is written
{\vec{x}}=4
ft
=1.22
m, where ft means
{\vec{a}}_{1}
and m means
{\vec{b}}_{1}
here.
NB: The qualitative vector
{\vec{x}}
is the same vector for all observers, not the quantitative values 4 or 1.22 (depends on a choice of a unit of measurement).

With duality notation:

{\vec{x}}=x^{1}{\vec{a}}_{1}=y^{1}{\vec{b}}_{1}
, so if
x^{1}=4
then
y^{1}\simeq 1.22
.

This manuscript insists on covariant objectivity; Thus an English engineer (and his foot) and a French engineer (and his metre) will be able to work together … and be able to avoid crashes like that of the Mars Climate Orbiter probe, see remark
A.14
. And they will be able to use the results of Galileo, Descartes, Newton, Euler… who used their own unit of length, and knew nothing about the metre defined in 1793 and adopted in 1799 in France (after 6 years of measurements), and considered by the scientific community at the end of the ninetieth century… and couldn’t explicitly use the “Euclidean dot products” either (which seems to have been defined mathematically by Grassmann around 1844).

1.3
Motion of an object

Let
{\it O\!b\!j}
be a “real object”, or “material object”, made of particles (e.g., the Moon: Exists independently of an observer). Let
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
,
t_{1}<t_{2}
.

Definition 1.2

The motion of
{\it O\!b\!j}
in
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
is the map

{\widetilde{\Phi}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr[t_{1},t_{2}]\times{\it O\!b\!j}&\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\cr(t,\underbrace{{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})}_{\hbox{\footnotesize particle}}&\rightarrow\underbrace{p={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})}_{\hbox{\footnotesize its position at $t$ in the Universe}}.\crcr}}\,\right.

(1.5)

And
t
is the time variable,
p
is the space variable, and
(t,p)\in\mathbb{R}\times{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
is the time-space variable.
And
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
is supposed to be
C^{2}
in time.

With an origin
{\cal O}
(observer dependent),
the motion can be described with the bi-point vector

{\vec{x}}=\overrightarrow{{\cal O}{\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})}=\overrightarrow{{\cal O}p}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\widetilde{\vec{\varphi}}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}).

(1.6)

But then, two observers with different origins
{\cal O}
and
{\cal O}_{b}
have different description of the motion. Therefore, in the following we won’t use
{\widetilde{\vec{\varphi}}}
.
Then (quantification) with a Cartesian basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
to make a referential
{\cal R}
, we get (
1.1
).

1.4
Virtual and real motion

Definition 1.3

A virtual (or possible) motion of
{\it O\!b\!j}
is a function
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
“regular enough for the calculations to be meaningful”.
Among all the virtual motions, the observed motion is called the real motion.

1.5
Hypotheses (Newton and Einstein)

Hypotheses of Newtonian mechanics (Galileo relativity) and general relativity (Einstein)
:

1- You can describe a phenomenon only at the actual time
t
and from the location
p
you are at
(you have
no
gift of ubiquity in time or space);

2- You don’t know the future;

3- You can use your memory, so use some past time
{t_{0}}
and some past position
{p_{t_{0}}}
;

4- You can use someone else memory (results of measurements) if you can communicate objectively.

1.6
Configurations

Fix
t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]
, and define

{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{t}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\it O\!b\!j}&\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\cr{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}&\mapsto p={\widetilde{\Phi}}_{t}({P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}):={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}).\cr}}\,\right.

Definition 1.4

The “configuration at
t
” of
{\it O\!b\!j}
is the range (or image) of
{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{t}
, i.e. is the subset of
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
(affine space) defined by

{\Omega_{t}}:=\{p\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}:\exists{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}\in{\it O\!b\!j}\hbox{ s{.}t{.}\ }p={\widetilde{\Phi}}_{t}({P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})\}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{t}({\it O\!b\!j}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\rm Im}({\widetilde{\Phi}}_{t}).

(1.7)

If
t
is the actual time then
{\Omega_{t}}
is the actual (or current or Eulerian) configuration.

If
t_{0}
is a time in the past then
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
is the past (or initial or Lagrangian) configuration.

Hypothesis:
At any time
t
,
{\Omega_{t}}
is supposed to be a “smooth domain” in
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
, and the map
{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{t}
is assumed to be one-to-one (= injective):
{\it O\!b\!j}
does not crash onto itself.

1.7
Definition of the Eulerian and Lagrangian variables

\bullet
If
t
is the actual time, then
{p_{t}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}_{t}({P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})\in{\Omega_{t}}
is called the Eulerian variable
relative to
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
and
t
.

\bullet
If
{t_{0}}
is a time in the past, then
{p_{t_{0}}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}_{t_{0}}({P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
is called the Lagrangian variable relative to
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
and
{t_{0}}
.
(A Lagrangian variable is a “past Eulerian variable”). (Two observers with two different origin of time
{t_{0}}
and
{t_{0}}^{\prime}
get two different Lagrangian variable while they have the same Eulerian variable.)

1.8
Trajectories

Let
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
be a motion of
{\it O\!b\!j}
, cf. (
1.5
),
and
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}\in{\it O\!b\!j}
(a particle in
{\it O\!b\!j}
= e.g. the Moon).

Definition 1.5

The (parametric) trajectory of
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
is the function

{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr[t_{1},t_{2}]&\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{n}},\cr t&\mapsto p(t)={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}(t):={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})\quad(\hbox{position of ${P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}$ at $t$ in the Universe}).\cr}}\,\right.

(1.8)

Its geometric trajectory is the range (image) of
{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}
, i.e.

\hbox{geometric trajectory of ${P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}$}:=\{q\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}:\exists t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]\hbox{ s{.}t{.}\ }q={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}(t)\}={\rm Im}({\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}})={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}([t_{1},t_{2}]).

(1.9)

1.9
Pointed vector, tangent space, fiber, vector field, bundle

(See e.g. Abraham–Marsden
[
3
]
.)
To deal with surfaces
S
in
{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
, e.g. with
S=
a sphere (and more generally with manifolds in
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
),
a vector cannot simply be a “bi-point vector connecting two points of
S
” (would get “through the surface”). A vector
is defined to be tangent to
S
: Consider a “regular” curve
c:s\in]-\varepsilon,\varepsilon[\rightarrow c(s)\in S
where
S
is a surface in an affine space, and the vector tangent to
S
at
c(0)
is
{\vec{w}}(c(0))=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{c(h)-c(0)\over h}
(it is defined with a parametrization of
c
in a general manifold); Considering all the possible curves, we get “all possible vectors on
S
”.

Notation:

T_{p}S:=\{\hbox{tangent vectors ${\vec{w}}_{p}$ at $S$ at\penalty\ $p$}\}=\hbox{The tangent space at\penalty\ $p\in S$}.

(1.10)

E.g., if
S
is a sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
p\in S
, then
T_{p}S
is its usual tangent plane at
p
at
S
.

E.g., particular case: If
S=\Omega
is an open set in
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
, then
T_{p}S=T_{p}\Omega={\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
is independent of
p
.

Definition 1.6

\hbox{The fiber at\penalty\ $p$}:=\{p\}\times T_{p}S=\{\underbrace{(p,{\vec{w}}_{p})}_{\hbox{\footnotesize pointed vector}}\in\{p\}\times T_{p}S\},

(1.11)

i.e., the fiber at
p
is the set of “pointed vectors at
p
”,
a pointed vector being the couple
(p,{\vec{w}}_{p})
made of the “base point”
p
and the vector
{\vec{w}}_{p}
defined at
p
.

Drawing: A vector in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
can be drawn anywhere in
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
; While a “pointed vectors at
p
” has to be drawn at the point
p
in
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
.

If the context is clear, a pointed vector is simply noted
{\widetilde{\vec{w}}}(p){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{w}}(p)
(lighten the writing).

Particular case: If
S=\Omega
is an open set in
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
, then the fiber at
p
is
T_{p}\Omega=\{p\}\times{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
.

Definition 1.7

\hbox{The tangent bundle }TS:=\bigcup_{p\in S}(\{p\}\times T_{p}S),

(1.12)

that is, is the union of the fibers.

Definition 1.8

A vector field
{\widetilde{\vec{w}}}
in
S
is a
C^{\infty}
function (or at least
C^{2}
in the following)

{\widetilde{\vec{w}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr S&\rightarrow TS\cr p&\rightarrow{\widetilde{\vec{w}}}(p)=(p,{\vec{w}}(p)).\cr}}\,\right.

(1.13)

If the context is clear, a vector field is simply noted
{\widetilde{\vec{w}}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{w}}
(lighten the writing).

2
Eulerian description (spatial description at actual time
t
)

2.1
The set of configurations

Let
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
be a motion of
{\it O\!b\!j}
, cf. (
1.5
),
and
\Omega_{t}={\widetilde{\Phi}}_{t}({\it O\!b\!j})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the configuration at
t
, cf. (
1.7
).
The set of configurations is the subset
\hbox{\large$\cal C$}\subset\mathbb{R}\times{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
(the “time-space”) defined by

\displaystyle{\hbox{\large$\cal C$}:=}

\displaystyle{{}\bigcup_{t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]}(\{t\}\times\Omega_{t})\quad(=\hbox{set in which you find particles in ``time-space''})}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}\{(t,p)\in\mathbb{R}\times{\mathbb{R}^{n}}:\exists(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})\in[t_{1},t_{2}]\times{\it O\!b\!j},\;p={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})\},}

(2.1)

Question: Why don’t we simply use
\bigcup_{t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]}{\Omega_{t}}
instead of
\hbox{\large$\cal C$}=\bigcup_{t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]}(\{t\}\times\Omega_{t})
?

Answer:
\cal C
gives the film of the life of
{\it O\!b\!j}
= the succession of the photos
{\Omega_{t}}
taken at each
t
;
And
{\Omega_{t}}
is obtained from
\cal C
thanks to the pause feature at
t
.
Whereas
\bigcup_{t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]}{\Omega_{t}}\subset{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
is the superposition of all the photos on the image
\bigcup_{t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]}{\Omega_{t}}
… and we don’t distinguish the past from the present.

2.2
Eulerian variables and functions

Definition 2.1

In short:
A Eulerian function relative to
{\it O\!b\!j}
is a function, with
m\in{\mathbb{N}^{*}}
,

{{\cal E}\!ul}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\hbox{\large$\cal C$}&\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}\hbox{ (or more generally a suitable set of tensors)}\cr(t,p)&\rightarrow{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,p),\crcr}}\,\right.

(2.2)

the spatial variable
p
being the Eulerian variable.

In details: A function
{{\cal E}\!ul}
being given as in (
2.2
),
the associated Eulerian function
\widehat{{\cal E}\!ul}
is the function

\widehat{{\cal E}\!ul}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\hbox{\large$\cal C$}&\rightarrow\hbox{\large$\cal C$}\times{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}\hbox{ (or $\hbox{\large$\cal C$}\times$ some suitable set of tensors)}\cr(t,p)&\rightarrow\widehat{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,p)=((t,p),{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,p))=(\hbox{time-space position\ ,\ value}),\crcr}}\,\right.

(2.3)

and is called “a field of functions”. So
\widehat{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,p)
is the “pointed
{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,p)
” at
(t,p)
(in time-space).

So, the range
{\rm Im}(\widehat{{\cal E}\!ul})=\widehat{{\cal E}\!ul}(\hbox{\large$\cal C$})
of an Eulerian function
\widehat{{\cal E}\!ul}
is the graph of
{{\cal E}\!ul}
.
(Recall: The graph of a function
f:x\in A\rightarrow f(x)\in B
is the subset
\{(x,f(x))\in A\times B\}\subset A\times B
: gives the “drawing of
f
”).

If there is no ambiguity,
\widehat{{\cal E}\!ul}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{{\cal E}\!ul}
for short.

At
t
, the Eulerian vector field at
t
is

\widehat{{\cal E}\!ul}_{t}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\Omega_{t}}&\rightarrow{\Omega_{t}}\times{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\cr p&\rightarrow\widehat{{\cal E}\!ul}_{t}(p):=(p,{{\cal E}\!ul}_{t}(p))=(\hbox{position\ ,\ value}).\crcr}}\,\right.

Example 2.2

{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,p)=\theta(t,p)\in\mathbb{R}=
temperature
of the particle
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
which is at
t
at
p={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
;

Example 2.3

{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,p)={\vec{u}}(t,p)\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}=
force applied on the particle
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}

which is at
t
at
p
.

Example 2.4

{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,p)=d{\vec{u}}(t,p)\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}:{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})=
the differential at
t
at
p
of a Eulerian function
{\vec{u}}
.

Question:
Why introduce
\widehat{{\cal E}\!ul}
? Isn’t
{{\cal E}\!ul}
sufficient?

Answer:

The “pointed value”
\widehat{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,p)=((t,p),{{\cal E}\!ul}((t,p)))
is drawn on the graph of
{{\cal E}\!ul}
.

E.g., at
t
at
p
the velocity vector
{\vec{v}}(t,p)\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}
can be drawn anywhere, while the “pointed vector”
\widehat{\vec{v}}(t,p)=((t,p);{\vec{v}}(t,p))
is
{\vec{v}}(t,p)
drawn at
t
at
p
(and
\widehat{\vec{v}}
is called the velocity field).

Moreover (
2.3
) emphasizes the difference between a Eulerian vector field and a Lagrangian vector function, see (
3.14
).

Remark 2.5

E.g., the initial framework of Cauchy for his description of forces is Eulerian: The Cauchy stress vector
\vec{t}={\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}.{\vec{n}}
is considered at the actual time
t
at a point
p\in{\Omega_{t}}
.
(It is not Lagrangian.)

2.3
Eulerian velocity (spatial velocity) and speed

Definition 2.6

In short:
Consider a particle
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
and its (regular) trajectory
{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}:t\rightarrow p(t)={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}(t)
, cf. (
1.8
).
Its Eulerian velocity at
t
at
p(t)={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}(t)
is

{\vec{v}}(t,p(t)):={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}{}^{\prime}(t){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\partial{\widetilde{\Phi}}\over\partial t}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}),\quad\hbox{when}\quad p(t)={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}(t),

(2.4)

i.e.,
{\vec{v}}(t,p(t))
is the tangent vector at
t
at
p(t)={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}(t)
to the trajectory
{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}
.
This defines the vector field (in short)

{\vec{v}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\hbox{\large$\cal C$}&\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\cr(t,{p_{t}})&\rightarrow{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}})\crcr}}\,\right\}
.

In details: cf. (
2.3
), the Eulerian velocity is
the function

\widehat{\vec{v}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\hbox{\large$\cal C$}&\rightarrow\hbox{\large$\cal C$}\times{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}\cr(t,p)&\rightarrow\widehat{\vec{v}}(t,p)=((t,p),{\vec{v}}(t,p))\crcr}}\,\right\}

(pointed vector) where
{\vec{v}}(t,p)
is given by (
2.4
).

Remark 2.7

{d{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}\over dt}(t)={\vec{v}}(t,{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}(t))
, with
p(t)={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}(t)
, is often written

{dp\over dt}(t)={\vec{v}}(t,p(t)),\quad\hbox{or}\quad{d{\vec{x}}\over dt}(t)={\vec{v}}(t,{\vec{x}}(t)),\quad\hbox{or}\quad{d{\vec{x}}\over dt}={\vec{v}}(t,{\vec{x}}),

(2.5)

the two last notations when an origin
O
is chosen and
{\vec{x}}(t)=\overrightarrow{{\cal O}p(t)}
. Such an equation is the prototype of an ODE (ordinary differential equation)
solved with the Cauchy–Lipschitz theorem, see §
5
. (A Lagrangian velocity does not produce an ODE, see (
3.21
).)

Definition 2.8

If an observer chooses a Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
(e.g. foot or metre built), the associated norm being
||.||_{g}
,
then the length
||{\vec{v}}(t,p)||_{g}
is the speed (or scalar velocity) of
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}

(e.g. in ft/s or in m/s).
And the context must remove the ambiguities: the “velocity” is
either the vector velocity
{\vec{v}}(t,p)={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}{}^{\prime}(t)
or the speed (the scalar velocity)
||{\vec{v}}(t,p)||_{g}
.

Exercice 2.9

Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
,

{\vec{x}}(t)=\overrightarrow{{\cal O}p(t)}
,

\vec{T}(t)={{\vec{x}}\,^{\prime}(t)\over||{\vec{x}}\,^{\prime}(t)||_{g}}
,
and
f(t)=||{\vec{x}}\,^{\prime}(t)||_{g}
(speed).
Prove :
{df\over dt}(t)=({\vec{x}}\,^{\prime\prime}(t),\vec{T}(t))_{g}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{x}}\,^{\prime\prime}(t)\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}\vec{T}(t)
(= tangential acceleration).

Answer
. 2-D and Euclidean basis:

{\vec{x}}(t)=\pmatrix{x(t)\cr y(t)}
gives

f(t)=(x^{\prime}(t)^{2}+y^{\prime}(t)^{2})^{1\over 2}
, thus

f^{\prime}(t)={x^{\prime}(t)x^{\prime\prime}(t)+y^{\prime}(t)y^{\prime\prime}(t)\over f(t)}={{{\vec{r}}\,^{\prime}}(t)\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{{\vec{r}}\,^{\prime\prime}}(t)\over||{{\vec{r}}\,^{\prime}}(t)||}
.
Idem in
n
-D.

2.4
Spatial derivative of the Eulerian velocity

t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]
is fixed,
{{\cal E}\!ul}
is a given Eulerian function, and

{{\cal E}\!ul}_{t}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\Omega_{t}}&\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}\cr p&\rightarrow{{\cal E}\!ul}_{t}(p):={{\cal E}\!ul}(t,p)\cr}}\,\right\}
is
C^{1}
.

2.4.1
Definition

Recall: If
\Omega
is an open set in
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
and if
f:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is differentiable at
p
, then its differential at
p
is the linear form
df(p)\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}};\mathbb{R})
(linear map with real values) defined by, for all
{\vec{u}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
(vector at
p
),

df(p).{\vec{u}}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{f(p{+}h{\vec{u}})-f(p)\over h}.

(2.6)

This expression is the same for all observers (English, French…: There is no inner dot product here).

Definition 2.10

The space derivative of
{{\cal E}\!ul}
at
(t,p)
is the differential
d{{\cal E}\!ul}_{t}
at
p
, i.e.,
for all
t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]
, all
p\in{\Omega_{t}}
and all
{\vec{w}}_{p}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
(vector at
p
),

(d{{\cal E}\!ul}_{t}(p).{\vec{w}}_{p}=)\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle d{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,p).{\vec{w}}_{p}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,p{+}h{\vec{w}}_{p})-{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,p)\over h}$}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial p}(t,p).{\vec{w}}_{p}.

(2.7)

In
{\Omega_{t}}
(the photo at
t
),
d{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,p).{\vec{w}}_{p}
gives the rate of variations of
{{\cal E}\!ul}_{t}
at
p
in the direction
{\vec{w}}_{p}
.

E.g., at
t
, the space derivative
d{\vec{v}}
of the Eulerian velocity field is defined by

d{\vec{v}}(t,p).{\vec{w}}_{p}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{{\vec{v}}(t,p{+}h{\vec{w}}_{p})-{\vec{v}}(t,p)\over h}\quad(=d{\vec{v}}_{t}(p).{\vec{w}}_{p}).

(2.8)

Remark 2.11

In differential geometry, (
2.6
) is also written
{\vec{u}}(f)(p)={d\over dh}f(p{+}h{\vec{u}})_{|h=0}
; Don’t use this notation if you are not at ease with differential geometry (where a vector is defined to be a derivation, so
{\vec{u}}[f]
is the derivation of
f
by
{\vec{u}}
).

2.4.2
The convective derivative
d{{\cal E}\!ul}.{\vec{v}}

Definition 2.12

If
{\vec{v}}
is the Eulerian velocity field, then
d{{\cal E}\!ul}.{\vec{v}}
is called the convective derivative of
{{\cal E}\!ul}
.

2.4.3
Quantification in a basis:
df.{\vec{u}}
is written
({\vec{u}}.{\vec{\rm grad}})f

Quantification:

Let
f:p\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rightarrow f(p)\in\mathbb{R}
be
C^{1}
.
Let
({\vec{e}}_{i})
be a basis in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
.
Let (usual definition)

{\partial f\over\partial x_{i}}(p):=df(p).{\vec{e}}_{i}\quad\hbox{and}\quad[df(p)]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{{\partial f\over\partial x_{1}}(p)&...&{\partial f\over\partial x_{n}}(p)}\;\hbox{ (line matrix)}.

(2.9)

(Recall: The matrix which represents a linear form is a line matrix.) And
[df(p)]_{|{\vec{e}}}
is the Jacobian matrix of
f
at
p
relative to
({\vec{e}}_{i})
.

So, with
{\vec{u}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}u_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
a vector at
p
, and with the usual matrix multiplication rule, we have

df(p).{\vec{u}}=[df(p)]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{u}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\partial f\over\partial x_{i}}(p)u_{i}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}u_{i}{\partial f\over\partial x_{i}}(p){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}({\vec{u}}.{\vec{\rm grad}})_{|e}f(p),

(2.10)

where
({\vec{u}}.{\vec{\rm grad}})_{|e}:C^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})\rightarrow C^{0}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
is the differential operator defined relative to a basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
by

({\vec{u}}.{\vec{\rm grad}})_{|e}(f)={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}u_{i}{\partial f\over\partial x_{i}}.

(2.11)

If the basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is unambiguously imposed, then
({\vec{u}}.{\vec{\rm grad}})_{|e}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{u}}.{\vec{\rm grad}}

For vector valued functions
{\vec{f}}:\Omega\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}
,
the above steps apply to the components of
{\vec{f}}
in a basis
({\vec{b}}_{i})
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}
: If
{\vec{f}}={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}f_{i}{\vec{b}}_{i}
,
i.e.
{\vec{f}}(p)={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}f_{i}(p){\vec{b}}_{i}
,
then

({\vec{u}}.{\vec{\rm grad}})_{|e}({\vec{f}})={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}(df_{i}.{\vec{u}}){\vec{b}}_{i}={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}(({\vec{u}}.{\vec{\rm grad}})_{|e}f_{i}){\vec{b}}_{i}={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}(u_{j}.{\partial f_{i}\over\partial x_{j}}){\vec{b}}_{i}.

(2.12)

2.4.4
Representation relative to a Euclidean dot product:
{\vec{\rm grad}}f

An observer chooses a distance unit (foot, metre…) and uses the associated Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
.

Let
\Omega
be an open set in
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
,
f\in C^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
(scalar valued function), and
p\in\Omega
.
Then the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Riesz representation vector of the differential form
df(p)
is called the gradient of
f
at
p
relative to
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
, and named
{\vec{\rm grad}}_{g}f(p)\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
: It is defined by

\forall{\vec{u}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}},\quad({\vec{\rm grad}}_{g}f(p),{\vec{u}})_{g}=df(p).{\vec{u}},\quad\hbox{written}\quad{\vec{\rm grad}}f\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{\vec{u}}=df.{\vec{u}},

(2.13)

the last notation iff a Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
is imposed to all observer (quite subjective: foot, metre ?).

(The first order Taylor expansion
f(p{+}h{\vec{u}})=f(p)+h\,df(p).{\vec{u}}+o(h)
can therefore, after a choice of an Euclidean dot product, be written
f(p{+}h{\vec{u}})=f(p)+h\,{\vec{\rm grad}}_{g}f(p)\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,{\vec{u}}+o(h)
.)

Quantification:
Let
({\vec{e}}_{i})
be a Cartesian basis in
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
.
Then (
2.13
) gives

[df].[{\vec{u}}]=[{\vec{\rm grad}}f]^{T}.[g].[{\vec{u}}],
for all
{\vec{u}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}

(more precisely
[df]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{u}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[{\vec{\rm grad}}_{g}f]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}.[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{u}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}
),
thus (since
[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}
is symmetric)

[{\vec{\rm grad}}f]=[g].[df]^{T}\quad\hbox{(column matrix)}.

(2.14)

I.e., if
{\vec{\rm grad}}f={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}a_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
then
a_{i}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}g_{ij}{\partial f\over\partial x_{j}}
for all
i
.
In particular, if
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-orthonormal basis then
[{\vec{\rm grad}}f]=[df]^{T}
.

With duality notations,
{\vec{\rm grad}}f={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}a^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
and (
2.14
) gives
a^{i}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}g_{ij}{\partial f\over\partial x^{j}}
: The Einstein convention is
not
satisfied (the index
j
is twice bottom), which is expected since the definition of
{\vec{\rm grad}}_{g}f
depends on a subjective choice (unit of length). In comparison,
df={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\partial f\over\partial x^{i}}dx^{i}
satisfies the Einstein convention (a differential is objective).

Mind the notations:
The gradient
{\vec{\rm grad}}_{g}f{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{\rm grad}}f
depends on
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
, cf. (
2.13
)-(
2.14
), while

({\vec{u}}.{\vec{\rm grad}})f
does not (only depends on a basis), cf. (
2.11
) (historical notations…).

2.4.5
Vector valued functions

For vector valued functions
{\vec{f}}:\Omega\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}
, the above steps apply to the components
f_{i}
of
{\vec{f}}

relative to a basis
({\vec{b}}_{i})
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}
… But, depending on the book you read:

1- Ambiguous:
d{\vec{f}}
, the differential of
{\vec{f}}
, is unfortunately also sometimes called the “gradient matrix” (although no Euclidean dot product is required).

2- Ambiguous: It could mean the differential…
or the Jacobian matrix… or its transposed… because an orthonormal basis relative to an imposed Euclidean dot product is chosen (which one?) and then
[{\vec{\rm grad}}f_{i}]=[df_{i}]^{T}
… And calculations confuses
[.]
and
[.]^{T}
…

3- Non ambiguous: In the objective framework of this manuscript, we will use the differential
d{\vec{f}}
(objective) to begin with; And only after an explicit choice of bases
({\vec{e}}_{i})
for quantitative purposes, the Jacobian matrix, which is

[df]_{|{\vec{e}}}
, will be used.

Exercice 2.13

A Euclidean framework being chosen, prove:

({\vec{v}}.{\vec{\rm grad}}){\vec{v}}={1\over 2}{\vec{\rm grad}}(||{\vec{v}}||^{2})+{\vec{\rm rot}}{\vec{v}}\wedge{\vec{v}}.

Answer
. Euclidean basis
({\vec{E}}_{i})
,
Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}(\cdot,\cdot)
, associated norm
||.||_{g}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}||.||
.
Thus
{\vec{v}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}v_{i}{\vec{E}}_{i}
gives
\displaystyle||{\vec{v}}||^{2}=\sum_{i}v_{i}^{2}
, thus

\displaystyle{\partial||{\vec{v}}||^{2}\over\partial x_{k}}=\sum_{i}2v_{i}{\partial v_{i}\over\partial x_{k}}
, for any
k=1,2,3
.
And, the first component of
{\vec{\rm rot}}{\vec{v}}
is
\displaystyle({\vec{\rm rot}}{\vec{v}})_{1}={\partial v_{3}\over\partial x_{2}}-{\partial v_{2}\over\partial x_{3}}
, idem for
({\vec{\rm rot}}{\vec{v}})_{2}
and
({\vec{\rm rot}}{\vec{v}})_{3}

(circular permutation). Thus (first component)

\displaystyle({\vec{\rm rot}}{\vec{v}}\wedge{\vec{v}})_{1}=({\partial v_{1}\over\partial x_{3}}-{\partial v_{3}\over\partial x_{1}})v_{3}-({\partial v_{2}\over\partial x_{1}}-{\partial v_{1}\over\partial x_{2}})v_{2}
, idem for
({\vec{\rm rot}}{\vec{v}}\wedge{\vec{v}})_{2}

and
({\vec{\rm rot}}{\vec{v}}\wedge{\vec{v}})_{2}
.
Thus
({1\over 2}{\vec{\rm grad}}(||{\vec{v}}||^{2})+{\vec{\rm rot}}{\vec{v}}\wedge{\vec{v}})_{1}=v_{1}{\partial v_{1}\over\partial x_{1}}+v_{2}{\partial v_{2}\over\partial x_{1}}+v_{3}{\partial v_{3}\over\partial x_{1}}+{\partial v_{1}\over\partial x_{3}}v_{3}-{\partial v_{3}\over\partial x_{1}}v_{3}-{\partial v_{2}\over\partial x_{1}}v_{2}+{\partial v_{1}\over\partial x_{2}}v_{2}=v_{1}{\partial v_{1}\over\partial x_{1}}+v_{2}{\partial v_{1}\over\partial x_{2}}+v_{3}{\partial v_{1}\over\partial x_{3}}=({\vec{v}}.{\vec{\rm grad}})v_{1}
. Idem for the other components.

2.5
Streamline (current line)

Fix
t\in\mathbb{R}
, and consider the photo
\Omega_{t}={\widetilde{\Phi}}_{t}({\it O\!b\!j})
.
Let
{p_{t}}\in{\Omega_{t}}
,
\varepsilon>0
, and consider the spatial curve in
{\Omega_{t}}
at
{p_{t}}
defined by:

c_{p_{t}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr]-\varepsilon,\varepsilon[&\rightarrow\Omega_{t}\cr s&\rightarrow q=c_{p_{t}}(s)\cr}}\,\right\}\quad\hbox{s{.}t{.}}\quad c_{p_{t}}(0)={p_{t}}.

(2.15)

So
s
is a curvilinear spatial coordinate (dimension of a length), and the graph of
c_{p_{t}}
is drawn in the photo
{\Omega_{t}}
at
t
.

Definition 2.14

{\vec{v}}:(t,p)\rightarrow{\vec{v}}(t,p)
being the Eulerian velocity field of
{\it O\!b\!j}
,
a streamline through a point
{p_{t}}\in{\Omega_{t}}
is a (parametric) spatial curve
c_{p_{t}}
solution of the differential equation

{dc_{p_{t}}\over ds}(s)={\vec{v}}_{t}(c_{p_{t}}(s))\quad\hbox{with}\quad c_{p_{t}}(0)={p_{t}}.

(2.16)

And
{\rm Im}(c_{p_{t}})
is the geometric associated streamline (
\subset{\Omega_{t}}
).

NB: (
2.16
) cannot be confused with (
2.5
):
In (
2.5
) the variable is the time variable
t
,
while in (
2.16
) the variable is the space variable
s
.

Usual notation:

If an origin
{\cal O}
is chosen at
t
by an observer and
{\vec{x}}(s):=\overrightarrow{{\cal O}c_{p_{t}}(s)}
, then (
2.16
) is written

{d{\vec{x}}\over ds}(s)={\vec{v}}_{t}({\vec{x}}(s))\quad\hbox{with}\quad{\vec{x}}(0)=\overrightarrow{O{p_{t}}}.

(2.17)

Moreover, with a Cartesian basis
({\vec{e}}_{i}))
chosen at
t
by the observer, with

{\vec{x}}(s)={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x_{i}(s){\vec{e}}_{i}
we get

{d{\vec{x}}\over ds}(s)={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{dx_{i}\over ds}(s){\vec{e}}_{i}
, and (
2.17
) reads as the differential system of
n
equations in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}

\forall i=1,...,n,\quad{dx_{i}\over ds}(s)=v_{i}(t,x_{1}(s),...,x_{n}(s))\quad\hbox{with}\quad x_{i}(0)=(\overrightarrow{O{p_{t}}})_{i}

(2.18)

(the
n
functions
x_{i}:s\rightarrow x_{i}(s)
are the unknown).
Also written

{dx_{1}\over v_{1}}=...={dx_{n}\over v_{n}}=ds,

(2.19)

which means: It is the differential system (
2.18
) of
n
equations and
n
unknowns which must be solved.

(With duality notations,

{dx^{i}\over ds}(s)=v^{i}(t,x^{1}(s),...,x^{n}(s))
and
x^{i}(0)=(\overrightarrow{O{p_{t}}})^{i}
for all
i
.)

2.6
Material time derivative (dérivées particulaires)

2.6.1
Usual definition

Goal: To compute the variations of a Eulerian function
{{\cal E}\!ul}
along the trajectory
{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}
of a particle
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}

(e.g. the temperature of a particle along its trajectory).
So consider the function
g_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
giving the values of
{{\cal E}\!ul}
relative to a
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
along its trajectory:

g_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}(t):={{\cal E}\!ul}(t,p(t))\quad\hbox{when}\quad p(t):={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}(t).

(2.20)

Definition 2.15

The Material time derivative of
{{\cal E}\!ul}
at
(t,p(t))
is
g_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}{}^{\prime}(t){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{D{{\cal E}\!ul}\over Dt}(t,p(t))
.

So:

{D{{\cal E}\!ul}\over Dt}(t,p(t)):=g_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}{}^{\prime}(t)\quad(=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{{{\cal E}\!ul}(t{+}h,p(t{+}h))-{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,p(t))\over h}).

(2.21)

Since
g_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}(t):={{\cal E}\!ul}(t,{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}(t))
we get

g_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}{}^{\prime}(t)={\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t}(t,{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}(t))+d{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}(t)).{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}^{\prime}(t)
, thus, having
{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}^{\prime}(t)={\vec{v}}(t,p(t))
(Eulerian velocity),

{D{{\cal E}\!ul}\over Dt}(t,p(t))={\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t}(t,p(t))+d{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,p(t)).{\vec{v}}(t,p(t))
:

\framebox{$\displaystyle{D{{\cal E}\!ul}\over Dt}:={\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t}+d{{\cal E}\!ul}.{\vec{v}}$}.

(2.22)

Exercice 2.16

Prove, if
{{\cal E}\!ul}
is
C^{2}
:

\displaystyle{{D^{2}{{\cal E}\!ul}\over Dt^{2}}=}

\displaystyle{{}{\partial^{2}{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t^{2}}+2d{\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t}.{\vec{v}}+d{{\cal E}\!ul}.{\partial{\vec{v}}\over\partial t}+d^{2}{{\cal E}\!ul}({\vec{v}},{\vec{v}})+d{{\cal E}\!ul}.d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{v}}}

(2.23)

Answer
.

\displaystyle{{D^{2}{{\cal E}\!ul}\over Dt^{2}}={D{D{{\cal E}\!ul}\over Dt}\over Dt}=g_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}^{\prime\prime}(t)=}

\displaystyle{{}{\partial({\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t}+d{{\cal E}\!ul}.{\vec{v}})\over\partial t}+d({\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t}+d{{\cal E}\!ul}.{\vec{v}}).{\vec{v}}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}{\partial^{2}{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t^{2}}+{\partial(d{{\cal E}\!ul})\over\partial t}.{\vec{v}}+d{{\cal E}\!ul}.{\partial{\vec{v}}\over\partial t}+d{\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t}.{\vec{v}}+d^{2}{{\cal E}\!ul}({\vec{v}},{\vec{v}})+d{{\cal E}\!ul}.d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{v}},}

and
{{\cal E}\!ul}

C^{2}
gives
{\partial\over\partial t}\circ d=d\circ{\partial\over\partial t}
(Schwarz theorem),
hence (
2.23
).

Exercice 2.17

Prove, if
{{\cal E}\!ul}
is
C^{2}
, for any vector field
{\vec{w}}
,

{D(d{{\cal E}\!ul}.{\vec{w}})\over Dt}=d{\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t}.{\vec{w}}+d{{\cal E}\!ul}.{\partial{\vec{w}}\over\partial t}+d^{2}{{\cal E}\!ul}({\vec{v}},{\vec{w}})+d{{\cal E}\!ul}.d{\vec{w}}.{\vec{v}}.

(2.24)

Answer
.
\displaystyle{D(d{{\cal E}\!ul}.{\vec{w}})\over Dt}={\partial(d{{\cal E}\!ul}.{\vec{w}})\over\partial t}+d(d{{\cal E}\!ul}.{\vec{w}}).{\vec{v}}={\partial d{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t}.{\vec{w}}+d{{\cal E}\!ul}.{\partial{\vec{w}}\over\partial t}+(d(d{{\cal E}\!ul}).{\vec{v}}).{\vec{w}}+d{{\cal E}\!ul}.d{\vec{w}}.{\vec{v}}
. And the Schwarz theorem gives
{\partial(d{{\cal E}\!ul})\over\partial t}=d({\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t})
since
{{\cal E}\!ul}\in C^{2}
.
Hence (
2.24
).

2.6.2
Remark: About notations

\bullet
The notation
{d\over dt}
(lowercase letters) concerns a function of one variable,
e.g.
{dg_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}\over dt}(t):=g_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}{}^{\prime}(t):=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{g_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}(t{+}h))-g_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}(t)\over h}
;

\bullet
The notation
{\partial\over\partial t}
concerns a function with more than one variable,
e.g.
{\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t}(t,p)=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{{{\cal E}\!ul}(t{+}h,p)-{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,p)\over h}
;

\bullet
The notation
{D\over Dt}
(capital letters) concerns a Eulerian function differentiated along a motion, cf. (
2.21
).

\bullet
Other notations, often practical but might be ambiguous if composed functions are considered:

{d{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,p(t))\over dt}:={D{{\cal E}\!ul}\over Dt}(t,p(t)),\quad\hbox{and}\quad{d{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,p(t))\over dt}_{|t={t_{0}}}:={D{{\cal E}\!ul}\over Dt}({t_{0}},p({t_{0}})).

(2.25)

2.6.3
Definition bis: Time-space definition

Consider a
C^{1}
time-space function
f:(t,p)\in\mathbb{R}\times{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rightarrow f(t,p)
where
t=
time and
p=
space.

Definition 2.18

The differential of
f:(t,p)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\rightarrow f(t,p)
considered as a function on the Cartesian (time
\times
space) product
\mathbb{R}\times{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
is called the “total differential”, or “total derivative”, and is written
Df

(here time and space are of a different nature).

So if
p_{+}=(t,p)\in\mathbb{R}\times{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
and
{\vec{w}}_{+}=(w_{0},{\vec{w}})\in\vec{\mathbb{R}}\times{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
(time
\times
space) then,
by definition of a differential,

Df(p_{+}).{\vec{w}}_{+}:=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{f(p_{+}+h{\vec{w}}_{+})-f(p_{+})\over h},

(2.26)

i.e.

Df(t,p).(w_{0},{\vec{w}}):=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{f(t{+}hw_{0},p{+}h{\vec{w}})-f(t,p)\over h}.

(2.27)

Thus

Df(t,p)={\partial f\over\partial t}(t,p)\,dt+df(t,p).

(2.28)

Along a trajectory
{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}:t\rightarrow p(t)={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}(t)
with
f={{\cal E}\!ul}
a Eulerian function:

Consider the time-space trajectory

\widetilde{\Psi}_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr[t_{1},t_{2}]&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\cr t&\rightarrow\widetilde{\Psi}_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}(t):=(t,{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}(t))\quad(=(t,p(t))).\crcr}}\,\right.

(2.29)

(So
{\rm Im}(\widetilde{\Psi}_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})=
graph
({\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}})
.)
The tangent vector to this curve at
t
is

\widetilde{\Psi}_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}{}^{\prime}(t)=(1,{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}{}^{\prime}(t))=(1,{\vec{v}}(t,p(t))\in\vec{\mathbb{R}}\times{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}

(2.30)

where
{\vec{v}}(t,p(t))
the Eulerian velocity at
p_{+}=(t,p(t))
.
And (
2.20
) reads

g_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}(t)=({{\cal E}\!ul}\circ\widetilde{\Psi}_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})(t)={{\cal E}\!ul}({\widetilde{\Psi}}_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}(t)),

(2.31)

thus

g_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}^{\prime}(t)=D{{\cal E}\!ul}({\widetilde{\Psi}}(t)).\widetilde{\Psi}_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}{}^{\prime}(t).

(2.32)

And we recover (
2.22
):

g_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}^{\prime}(t)\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqDxi01})}{\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t}(t,p(t)).1+d{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,p(t)).{\vec{v}}(t,p(t)){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{D{{\cal E}\!ul}\over Dt}(t,p(t)):

The material time derivative is the “total derivative”
D{{\cal E}\!ul}

along the time-space trajectory
\widetilde{\Psi}_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
.

2.6.4
The material time derivative is a derivation

Proposition 2.19

All the functions are Eulerian and supposed
C^{1}
.

\bullet
Linearity:

{D({{\cal E}\!ul}_{1}+\lambda{{\cal E}\!ul}_{2})\over Dt}={D{{\cal E}\!ul}_{1}\over Dt}+\lambda{D{{\cal E}\!ul}_{2}\over Dt}.

(2.33)

\bullet
Product rules:
If
{{\cal E}\!ul}_{1},{{\cal E}\!ul}_{2}
are scalar valued functions then

{D({{\cal E}\!ul}_{1}{{\cal E}\!ul}_{2})\over Dt}={D{{\cal E}\!ul}_{1}\over Dt}\,{{\cal E}\!ul}_{2}+{{\cal E}\!ul}_{1}\,{D{{\cal E}\!ul}_{2}\over Dt}.

(2.34)

And if
{\vec{w}}
is a vector field and
T
a compatible tensor (so
T.{\vec{w}}
is meaningful) then

{D(T.{\vec{w}})\over Dt}={DT\over Dt}.{\vec{w}}+T.{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}.

(2.35)

Proof.
Let
i=1,2
, and
g_{i}
defined by
g_{i}(t):={{\cal E}\!ul}_{i}(t,p(t))
where
p(t)={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}(t)
.

\bullet

(g_{1}+\lambda g_{2})^{\prime}=g_{1}^{\prime}+\lambda g_{2}^{\prime}
gives (
2.33
).

\bullet
On the one hand
{D(T.{\vec{w}})\over Dt}={\partial(T.{\vec{w}})\over\partial t}+d(T.{\vec{w}}).{\vec{v}}={\partial T\over\partial t}.{\vec{w}}+T.{\partial{\vec{w}}\over\partial t}+(dT.{\vec{v}}).{\vec{w}}+T.(d{\vec{w}}.{\vec{v}})
,
and on the other hand

{DT\over Dt}.{\vec{w}}+T.{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}=({\partial T\over\partial t}+dT.{\vec{v}}).{\vec{w}}+T.({\partial{\vec{w}}\over\partial t}+d{\vec{w}}.{\vec{v}})
. Thus (
2.34
)-(
2.35
).

2.6.5
Commutativity issue

The Schwarz theorem that, when
{{\cal E}\!ul}
is
C^{2}
, the derivatives
{\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t}
and
d{{\cal E}\!ul}
commute.
But

Proposition 2.20

The material time derivative
{D\over Dt}
does not commute with the temporal derivation
{\partial\over\partial t}

or with the spatial derivation
d
: We have

{\partial({D{{\cal E}\!ul}\over Dt})\over\partial t}\neq{D({\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t})\over Dt}

and
d({D{{\cal E}\!ul}\over Dt})\neq{D(d{{\cal E}\!ul})\over Dt}
in general (because the variables
t
and
p
are not independent along a trajectory). In facts:

\left.\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\partial({D{{\cal E}\!ul}\over Dt})\over\partial t}=&{D({\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t})\over Dt}+d{{\cal E}\!ul}.{\partial{\vec{v}}\over\partial t}\cr=&{\partial^{2}{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t^{2}}+d{\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t}.{\vec{v}}+d{{\cal E}\!ul}.{\partial{\vec{v}}\over\partial t}\crcr}}\,\right\},\quad\hbox{and}\quad\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr d({D{{\cal E}\!ul}\over Dt})=&{D(d{{\cal E}\!ul})\over Dt}+d{{\cal E}\!ul}.d{\vec{v}}\cr=&{\partial(d{{\cal E}\!ul})\over\partial t}+d^{2}{{\cal E}\!ul}.{\vec{v}}+d{{\cal E}\!ul}.d{\vec{v}}\crcr}}\,\right.

(2.36)

Proof.
\displaystyle{\partial{D{{\cal E}\!ul}\over Dt}\over\partial t}={\partial({\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t}+d{{\cal E}\!ul}.{\vec{v}})\over\partial t}={\partial({\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t})\over\partial t}+{\partial d{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t}.{\vec{v}}+d{{\cal E}\!ul}.{\partial{\vec{v}}\over\partial t}\mathop{=}^{\rm Schwarz}{\partial({\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t})\over\partial t}+d{\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t}.{\vec{v}}+d{{\cal E}\!ul}.{\partial{\vec{v}}\over\partial t}
, thus (
2.36
)
1
.

\displaystyle d{D{{\cal E}\!ul}\over Dt}=d({\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t}+d{{\cal E}\!ul}.{\vec{v}})={\partial(d{{\cal E}\!ul})\over\partial t}+d(d{{\cal E}\!ul}).{\vec{v}}+d{{\cal E}\!ul}.d{\vec{v}}
,
thus (
2.36
)
2
.

So
\displaystyle{\partial({D{{\cal E}\!ul}\over Dt})\over\partial t}\neq{D({\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t})\over Dt}

and
\displaystyle d({D{{\cal E}\!ul}\over Dt})\neq{D(d{{\cal E}\!ul})\over Dt}

in general.

Exercice 2.21

Prove (
2.36
) with components.

Answer
.
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is a Cartesian basis.

{\partial{D{{\cal E}\!ul}\over Dt}\over\partial t}={\partial({\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t}+\sum_{i}{\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial x^{i}}.v^{i})\over\partial t}={\partial^{2}{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t^{2}}+\sum_{i}{\partial^{2}{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t\partial x^{i}}.v^{i}+\sum_{i}{\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial x^{i}}.{\partial v^{i}\over\partial t}={\partial^{2}{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t^{2}}+\sum_{i}{\partial^{2}{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t\partial x^{i}}.v^{i}+d{{\cal E}\!ul}.{\partial{\vec{v}}\over\partial t}
.
And

{D({\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t})\over Dt}={\partial^{2}{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t^{2}}+\sum_{i}{\partial{\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t}\over\partial x^{i}}.v^{i}={\partial^{2}{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t^{2}}+\sum_{i}{\partial^{2}{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t\partial x^{i}}.v^{i}
.

And
d({D{{\cal E}\!ul}\over Dt}).{\vec{w}}=\sum_{j}{\partial{D{{\cal E}\!ul}\over Dt}\over\partial x^{j}}w^{j}=\sum_{j}{\partial({\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t}+\sum_{i}{\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial x^{i}}v^{i})\over\partial x^{j}}w^{j}=\sum_{j}{\partial^{2}{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t\partial x^{j}}w^{j}+\sum_{ij}{\partial^{2}{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial x^{i}\partial x^{j}}v^{i}w^{j}+\sum_{ij}{\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial x^{i}}{\partial v^{i}\over\partial x^{j}}w^{j}=\sum_{j}{\partial^{2}{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t\partial x^{j}}w^{j}+d^{2}{{\cal E}\!ul}({\vec{v}},{\vec{w}})+d{{\cal E}\!ul}.d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}
.
And
{D(d{{\cal E}\!ul})\over Dt}.{\vec{w}}=({\partial(d{{\cal E}\!ul})\over\partial t}+d(d{{\cal E}\!ul}).{\vec{v}}).{\vec{w}}={\partial(d{{\cal E}\!ul})\over\partial t}.{\vec{w}}+d^{2}{{\cal E}\!ul}({\vec{v}},{\vec{w}})=\sum_{i}{\partial^{2}{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial x^{i}\partial t}\,w^{i}+d^{2}{{\cal E}\!ul}({\vec{v}},{\vec{w}})
.
Thus
d({D{{\cal E}\!ul}\over Dt}).{\vec{w}}={D(d{{\cal E}\!ul})\over Dt}.{\vec{w}}+d{{\cal E}\!ul}.d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}
for all
{\vec{w}}
.

2.7
Eulerian acceleration

Definition 2.22

In short: If
{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}
is
C^{2}
, then the Eulerian acceleration of the particle
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
which is at
t
at
{p_{t}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
is

{\vec{\gamma}}(t,{p_{t}}):={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}{}^{\prime\prime}(t){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\partial^{2}{\widetilde{\Phi}}\over\partial t^{2}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}).

(2.37)

In details: as in (
2.3
), the Eulerian acceleration (vector) field
\widehat{\vec{\gamma}}
is defined with (
2.37
) by

\widehat{\vec{\gamma}}(t,{p_{t}})=((t,{p_{t}}),{\vec{\gamma}}(t,{p_{t}}))\in\hbox{\large$\cal C$}\times{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}\quad\hbox{(pointed vector)}.

(2.38)

Proposition 2.23

\framebox{$\displaystyle{\vec{\gamma}}={D{\vec{v}}\over Dt}={\partial{\vec{v}}\over\partial t}+d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{v}}$}.

(2.39)

And if
{\vec{v}}
is
C^{2}
then

\displaystyle{{}d{\vec{\gamma}}={\partial(d{\vec{v}})\over\partial t}+d^{2}{\vec{v}}.{\vec{v}}+d{\vec{v}}.d{\vec{v}}={D(d{\vec{v}})\over Dt}+d{\vec{v}}.d{\vec{v}}.}

(2.40)

Proof.
With
g(t)={\vec{v}}(t,p(t))={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}{}^{\prime}(t)
and (
2.22
) we get

{\vec{\gamma}}(t,p(t))=g^{\prime}(t)={D{\vec{v}}\over Dt}(t,p(t))
.
And
{\vec{v}}
being
C^{2}
, the Schwarz theorem gives
d{\partial{\vec{v}}\over\partial t}={\partial(d{\vec{v}})\over\partial t}
.

Definition 2.24

If an observer chooses a Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
(based on a foot, a metre…), the associated norm being
||.||_{g}
,
then the length
||{\vec{\gamma}}(t,{p_{t}})||_{g}
is the (scalar) acceleration of
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
.

2.8
Time Taylor expansion of
{\widetilde{\Phi}}

Let
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}\in{\it O\!b\!j}
and
t\in]t_{1},t_{2}[
. Suppose
{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}\in C^{2}(]t_{1},t_{2}[;{\mathbb{R}^{n}})
. Its second-order (time) Taylor expansion of
{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}
is, in the vicinity of a
t\in]t_{1},t_{2}[
,

\displaystyle{{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}(\tau)=}

\displaystyle{{}{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}(t)+(\tau{-}t){\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}^{\prime}(t)+{(\tau{-}t)^{2}\over 2}{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}^{\prime\prime}(t)+o((\tau{-}t)^{2}),}

(2.41)

i.e.

p(\tau)=p(t)+(\tau{-}t){\vec{v}}(t,p(t))+{(\tau{-}t)^{2}\over 2}{\vec{\gamma}}(t,p(t))+o((\tau{-}t)^{2}).

(2.42)

3
Motion from an initial configuration: Lagrangian description

Instead of working on
{\it O\!b\!j}
, an observer may prefer to work with an initial configuration
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}({t_{0}},{\it O\!b\!j})
of
{\it O\!b\!j}
(essential for elasticity): This is the “Lagrangian approach”. This Lagrangian approach is not objective: Two observers may choose two different initial (times and) configurations.

3.1
Initial configuration and Lagrangian “motion”

3.1.1
Definition

{\it O\!b\!j}
is a material object,
{\widetilde{\Phi}}:[t_{1},t_{2}[\times{\it O\!b\!j}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
is its motion,
{\Omega_{\tau}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\tau}({\it O\!b\!j})
is its configuration at
\tau
,

{t_{0}}\in]t_{1},t_{2}[
is an “initial time”, and
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
is the initial configuration for the observer who chose
{t_{0}}
.

Definition 3.1

The motion of
{\it O\!b\!j}
relative to the initial configuration
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}({t_{0}},{\it O\!b\!j})
is the function

{\Phi^{t_{0}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr[t_{1},t_{2}]\times{\Omega_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\cr(t,{p_{t_{0}}})&\mapsto{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}):={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})\quad\hbox{when}\quad{p_{t_{0}}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}({t_{0}},{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}).\crcr}}\,\right.

(3.1)

So,
{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}):={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
is the position at
t
of the particle
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}

which was at
{p_{t_{0}}}
at
{t_{0}}
. In particular
{p_{t_{0}}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}}):={\widetilde{\Phi}}({t_{0}},{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
.

Marsden and Hughes notations: Once an initial time
{t_{0}}
has been chosen by an observer,
then
{\Phi^{t_{0}}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\Phi
,
then
{p_{t_{0}}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}P
(capital letter for positions at
{t_{0}}
)
and
{p_{t}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}p
(lowercase letter for positions at
t
), so

p=\Phi(t,P)\;\in{\Omega_{t}}.

(3.2)

(When objectivity is under concern, we need to switch back to the notations
{\Phi^{t_{0}}}
,
{p_{t_{0}}}
and
{p_{t}}
.)

NB:

\bullet
Talking about the motion of a position
{p_{t_{0}}}
is absurd: A position in
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
does not move. Thus
{\Phi^{t_{0}}}
has no existence without the definition, at first,
of the motion
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
of particles.

\bullet
The domain of definition of
{\Phi^{t_{0}}}
depends on
{t_{0}}
through
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
:
The superscript
{}^{t_{0}}
recalls it.
And a late observer with initial time
{t_{0}}^{\prime}>{t_{0}}
defines
\Phi^{{t_{0}}^{\prime}}

which domain of definition is
[t_{1},t_{2}]\times\Omega_{{t_{0}}^{\prime}}
;
And
\Phi^{{t_{0}}^{\prime}}\neq{\Phi^{t_{0}}}
in general because
\Omega_{{t_{0}}^{\prime}}\neq{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
in general.

\bullet
The following notation is also used:

{\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})=\Phi(t;{t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}}).

(3.3)

(The couple
({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})
is “the initial condition”, or
{t_{0}}
and
{p_{t_{0}}}
are the initial conditions, see the § on flows).

\bullet
If a origin
{\cal O}\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
is chosen by the observer, we may also use, with (
1.6
),

{\vec{x}}_{t_{0}}=\overrightarrow{{\cal O}{p_{t_{0}}}}={{\vec{\varphi}}^{\,t_{0}}}({t_{0}},{\vec{x}}_{t_{0}})={\vec{X}}=\overrightarrow{{\cal O}P}\quad\hbox{and}\quad{\vec{x}}_{t}=\overrightarrow{{\cal O}{p_{t}}}={{\vec{\varphi}}^{\,t_{0}}}(t,{\vec{x}}_{t_{0}})={\vec{x}}=\overrightarrow{{\cal O}p}.

(3.4)

3.1.2
Diffeomorphism between configurations

With (
3.1
), define

{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\Omega_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow{\Omega_{t}}\cr{p_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}):={\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}).\crcr}}\,\right.

(3.5)

Hypothesis:
For all
{t_{0}},t\in]t_{1},t_{2}[
, the map
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}:{\Omega_{t_{0}}}\rightarrow{\Omega_{t}}
is a
C^{k}
diffeomorphism
(a
C^{k}
invertible function whose inverse is
C^{k}
), where
k\in{\mathbb{N}^{*}}
depends on the required regularity.

Thus (
3.5
) gives
{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{t}({P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({\widetilde{\Phi}}_{t_{0}}({P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}))
, true for all
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}\in{\it O\!b\!j}
,
thus
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}\circ{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{t_{0}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}_{t}
,
i.e.

\framebox{$\displaystyle{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}:={\widetilde{\Phi}}_{t}\circ({\widetilde{\Phi}}_{t_{0}})^{-1}$}.

(3.6)

Thus,
{\Phi_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}}}=I
and
\Phi^{t}_{t_{0}}\circ{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}=({\widetilde{\Phi}}_{t}\circ({\widetilde{\Phi}}_{t_{0}})^{-1})\circ({\widetilde{\Phi}}_{t_{0}}\circ({\widetilde{\Phi}}_{t})^{-1})=I
give

\Phi^{t}_{t_{0}}=({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{-1}.

(3.7)

3.1.3
Trajectories

Let
({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})\in[t_{1},t_{2}]\times{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
(initial conditions) and with (
3.1
) define

{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr[t_{1},t_{2}]&\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\cr t&\mapsto p(t)={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t):={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}(t)={\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})\quad\hbox{when}\quad{p_{t_{0}}}={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}(t_{0}).\crcr}}\,\right.

(3.8)

Definition 3.2

{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}
is called the (parametric) “trajectory of
{p_{t_{0}}}
”, which means:
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}
is the trajectory of the particle
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
that is located at
{p_{t_{0}}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
at
{t_{0}}
. And the geometric “trajectory of
{p_{t_{0}}}
” is

{\rm Im}({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}})={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}([t_{1},t_{2}])=\bigcup_{t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]}\{{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t)\}\quad(={\rm Im}({\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}})).

(3.9)

NB: The terminology “trajectory of
{p_{t_{0}}}
” is awkward, since a position
{p_{t_{0}}}
does not move: It is indeed the trajectory
{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}
of a particle
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
which is at
{p_{t_{0}}}
at
{t_{0}}
that must be understood.

3.1.4
Streaklines (lignes d’émission)

Take a film between
{t_{0}}
and
T
(start and end).

Definition 3.3

Let
Q
be a fixed point in
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
(you see the point
Q
on each photo that make up the film).
The streakline through
Q
is the set

\displaystyle{E_{{t_{0}},T}(Q)=}

\displaystyle{{}\{p\in\Omega:\exists\tau\in[{t_{0}},T]:p=\Phi^{\tau}_{T}(Q)=(\Phi^{T}_{\tau})^{-1}(Q)\}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}\{p\in\Omega:\exists u\in[0,T{-}{t_{0}}]:p=\Phi^{T-u}_{T}(Q)=(\Phi^{T}_{T-u})^{-1}(Q)\}}

(3.10)

= the set at
T
of the positions (a line in
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
) of all the particles which were at
Q
at a
\tau\in[{t_{0}},T]
.

Example 3.4

Smoke comes out of a chimney.
Fix a camera nearby, choose a point
Q
at the top of the chimney where the particles are colored, and make a film.
At
T
stop filming.
Then (at time
T
) superimpose the photos in the film: The colored curve we see is the streakline.

In other words

=\bigcup_{\tau\in[{t_{0}},T]}\{\Phi_{Q}^{\tau}(T)\}=\bigcup_{u\in[0,T{-}{t_{0}}]}\{\Phi_{Q}^{T{-}u}(T)\}.

3.2
Lagrangian variables and functions

3.2.1
Definition

Consider a motion
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
, cf. (
1.5
).
An observer chose (subjective) a
{t_{0}}\in[t_{1},t_{2}]
(“in the past”); So
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}({t_{0}},{\it O\!b\!j})
is his initial configuration.
Let
m\in{\mathbb{N}^{*}}
.

Definition 3.5

In short: A Lagrangian function, relative to
{\it O\!b\!j}
,
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
and
{t_{0}}
, is a function

{{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr[t_{1},t_{2}]\times{\Omega_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}\ \hbox{ (or, more generally, some adequat set)}\cr(t,{p_{t_{0}}})&\rightarrow{{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}),\crcr}}\,\right.

(3.11)

and
{p_{t_{0}}}
is called the Lagrangian variable relative to the (subjective) choice
{t_{0}}
.

(To compare with (
2.2
): A Eulerian function does not depend on any
{t_{0}}
.)

Example 3.6

Scalar values:

{{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})=\Theta^{t_{0}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})=
temperature at
t
at
{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})

of the particle
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
that was at
{p_{t_{0}}}
at
{t_{0}}
.
(So, continuing example
2.2
,
\Theta^{t_{0}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})=\theta(t,{p_{t}})
.)

Example 3.7

Vectorial values:

{{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})={\vec{U}}^{t_{0}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})=
force at
t
at
{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})

acting on the particle
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
that was at
{p_{t_{0}}}
at
{t_{0}}
.
(So, continuing example
2.3
,
{\vec{U}}^{t_{0}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})={\vec{u}}(t,{p_{t}})
.)

If
t
is fixed or if
{p_{t_{0}}}\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
is fixed, then we define

{{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}_{t}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\Omega_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}\hbox{ (or, more generally, some adequat set)}\quad\cr{p_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow{{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}):={{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}),\crcr}}\,\right.

(3.12)

{{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr[t_{1},t_{2}]&\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}\hbox{ (or, more generally, some adequat set)}\cr t&\rightarrow{{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t):={{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}).\crcr}}\,\right.

(3.13)

Remark 3.8

The position
{p_{t_{0}}}
is also sometimes called a “material point”, which is counter intuitive:

{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
(objective) is the material point, and
{p_{t_{0}}}
is just its spatial position at
{t_{0}}
(subjective); And a Eulerian variable
{p_{t}}
is not called a “material point” at
t
…

By the way, the variable
{p_{t}}
is also called the “updated Lagrangian variable”…

3.2.2
A Lagrangian function is a two point tensor

Definition 3.9

In details:
{{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}}
being defined in (
3.11
),
a Lagrangian function is a function

{\widetilde{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr[t_{1},t_{2}]\times{\Omega_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow\hbox{\large$\cal C$}\times{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}\cr(t,{p_{t_{0}}})&\rightarrow{\widetilde{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})=((t,{p_{t}}),{{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}))\quad\hbox{when}\quad{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).\crcr}}\,\right.

(3.14)

I.e.
{\widetilde{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})=((t,{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})),{{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}))
. (And
{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}
can be replaced by some set.)

Definition 3.10

(Marsden and Hughes
[
14
]
.) A Lagrangian function is a “two point vector field” (or more generally a “two point tensor”) in reference to the points
{p_{t_{0}}}\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
(departure set) and
{p_{t}}\in{\Omega_{t}}
(arrival set) where the value
{{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})
is considered.

Interpretation:
(
3.14
) tells that
{{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})
is
not
represented at
(t,{p_{t_{0}}})
, but at
(t,{p_{t}})
: That is, having

\hbox{\rm graph}({{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}})=\{((t,{p_{t_{0}}}),{{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}))\quad\hbox{and}\quad{\rm Im}({\widetilde{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}})=\{((t,{p_{t}}),{{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}))\},

(3.15)

we have

{\rm Im}({\widetilde{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}})\neq\hbox{\rm graph}({{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}}):

(3.16)

So a Lagrangian function does
not
define a tensor in the usual sense. To compare with the Eulerian function
{{\cal E}\!ul}
which defines a tensor (in particular
{\rm Im}(\widehat{{\cal E}\!ul})=\hbox{\rm graph}({{\cal E}\!ul})
), cf. (
2.3
).

3.3
Lagrangian function associated with a Eulerian function

3.3.1
Definition

Let
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
be a motion, cf. (
1.5
).
Let
{{\cal E}\!ul}
be a Eulerian function, cf. (
2.3
). Let
{t_{0}}\in[t_{1},t_{2}]
.

Definition 3.11

The Lagrangian function
{{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}}
associated with the Eulerian function
{{\cal E}\!ul}
is defined by,
for all
(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})\in[t_{1},t_{2}]\times{\it O\!b\!j}
,

{{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}}(t,{\widetilde{\Phi}}({t_{0}},{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})):={{\cal E}\!ul}(t,{\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})),

(3.17)

i.e., for all
(t,{p_{t_{0}}})\in[t_{1},t_{2}]\times{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
,

{{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}):={{\cal E}\!ul}(t,{p_{t}}),\quad\hbox{when}\quad{p_{t}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t}})

(3.18)

i.e.,

{{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}):={{\cal E}\!ul}(t,{p_{t}})
when
{p_{t_{0}}}=({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{-1}({p_{t}})
for all
(t,{p_{t}})\in\hbox{\large$\cal C$}
.
In other words:

\framebox{$\displaystyle{{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}_{t}}:={{\cal E}\!ul}_{t}\circ{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}$}.

(3.19)

3.3.2
Remarks

\bullet

If you have a Lagrangian function, then you can associate the function

{{\cal E}\!ul}_{t}^{t_{0}}:={{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}_{t}}\circ({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{-1}

(3.20)

which thus a priori depends on
{t_{0}}
.
But, a Eulerian function is independent of any initial time
{t_{0}}
.

\bullet
For one measurement, there is only one Eulerian function
{{\cal E}\!ul}
,
while there are as many associated Lagrangian function
{{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}}
as they are
{t_{0}}
(as many as observers):
The Lagrangian function
{{\cal L}ag}^{{t_{0}}^{\prime}}
of a late observer who chooses
{t_{0}}^{\prime}>{t_{0}}

is different from
{{{\cal L}ag}^{t_{0}}}
since the domains of definition
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
and
\Omega_{{t_{0}}^{\prime}}

are different (in general).

3.4
Lagrangian velocity

3.4.1
Definition

Definition 3.12

In short: The Lagrangian velocity at
t
at
{p_{t}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
of the particle
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
is
the function

{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\mathbb{R}\times{\Omega_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\cr(t,{p_{t_{0}}})&\rightarrow{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}):={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}{}^{\prime}(t)\quad\hbox{when}\quad{p_{t_{0}}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}({t_{0}},{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}).\crcr}}\,\right.

(3.21)

In details: With (
3.21
), the Lagrangian velocity is the two point vector field given by

\widehat{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}):\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\mathbb{R}\times{\Omega_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow\hbox{\large$\cal C$}\times{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\cr(t,{p_{t_{0}}})&\rightarrow\widehat{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}):=((t,{p_{t}}),{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})),\quad\hbox{when}\quad{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}).\crcr}}\,\right.

(3.22)

Thus
{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}{}^{\prime}(t)={\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}})
is the velocity at
t
at
{p_{t}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
of the particle
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}

which was at
{p_{t_{0}}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}({p_{t_{0}}},{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
at
{t_{0}}
; And
{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})
is
not
tangent to
\hbox{\rm graph}({{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}})
, cf. (
3.16
): It is tangent to
\hbox{\rm graph}({\vec{v}})
at
(t,{p_{t}})
.

If
t
is fixed, or if
{p_{t_{0}}}\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
is fixed, then we define

{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}):={{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}),\quad\hbox{or}\quad{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}(t):={{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}).

(3.23)

Remark:
A usual definition is given without explicit reference to a particle; It is, instead of (
3.21
),

{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}):={\partial{\Phi^{t_{0}}}\over\partial t}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}),\quad\forall(t,{p_{t_{0}}})\in\mathbb{R}\times{\Omega_{t_{0}}}.

(3.24)

3.4.2
Lagrangian velocity versus Eulerian velocity

(
3.21
) and (
2.4
) give (alternative definition), with
{p_{\tau}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}(\tau,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
,

{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})={\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}})\quad(={\partial{\Phi^{t_{0}}}\over\partial t}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}{}^{\prime}(t)=\hbox{velocity at $t$ at ${p_{t}}$ of ${P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}$}).

(3.25)

In other words,

\framebox{$\displaystyle{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}={\vec{v}}_{t}\circ{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}$}.

(3.26)

3.4.3
Relation between differentials

For
C^{2}
motions (
3.26
) gives

d{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})=d{\vec{v}}_{t}({p_{t}}).d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})\quad\hbox{when}\quad{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).

(3.27)

I.e., with

{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\hbox{the deformation gradient relative to\penalty\ ${t_{0}}$ and\penalty\ $t$},

(3.28)

\framebox{$\displaystyle d{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})=d{\vec{v}}_{t}({p_{t}}).{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})$}\quad\hbox{when}\quad{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).

(3.29)

Abusively written (dangerous notation: At what points, relative to what times?)

d{\vec{V}}=d{\vec{v}}.F.

(3.30)

3.4.4
Computation of
d{\vec{v}}
called
L=\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptF}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}.F^{-1}
wih Lagrangian variables

The Lagrangian approach can be introduced before the Eulerian approach:
{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}}
being given, define

{\vec{v}}^{t_{0}}(t,{p_{t}}):={{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}),\quad\hbox{when}\quad{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}),

(3.31)

cf. (
3.20
). (I.e.
{\vec{v}}^{t_{0}}(t,{p_{t}}):={{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}}(t,{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}^{-1}({p_{t}}))
).
So
{\vec{v}}^{t_{0}}(t,{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}))={\partial{\Phi^{t_{0}}}\over\partial t}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})
, thus

d{\vec{v}}^{t_{0}}(t,{p_{t}}).d{\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})=d({\partial{\Phi^{t_{0}}}\over\partial t})(t,{p_{t_{0}}})={\partial(d{\Phi^{t_{0}}})\over\partial t}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})={\partial{F^{t_{0}}}\over\partial t}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}),

(3.32)

when
{\Phi^{t_{0}}}
is
C^{2}
and
{F^{t_{0}}}:=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}}
. Thus

d{\vec{v}}^{t_{0}}(t,{p_{t}})={\partial{F^{t_{0}}}\over\partial t}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}).{F^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})^{-1},\quad\hbox{written in short}\quad d{\vec{v}}=\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptF}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}.F^{-1}\quad\mathrel{(}\hbox{points? times?}).

(3.33)

And
d{\vec{v}}^{t_{0}}_{t}
can be written
L^{t_{0}}_{t}
in classical mechanics books, so you can find

{L^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t}}):=\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt{F^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}(t).{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})^{-1},\quad\mathrel{\hbox{written in short}}\quad\mathrel{L}=\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptF}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}.F^{-1}\quad\mathrel{(}\hbox{at what points, what times?}).

(3.34)

Here it is not obvious that
{L^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t}})
does
not
depend on
{t_{0}}
, which is indeed the case, cf. (
3.29
):

{L^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t}})=d{\vec{v}}_{t}({p_{t}}).

(3.35)

Reminder: if possible, use Eulerian quantities as long as possible
1
1

1

To get Eulerian results from Lagrangian computations
can make the understanding of a Lie derivative quite difficult: To introduce the “so-called” Lie derivatives in classical mechanics you can find the following steps:
1- At
t
consider the Cauchy stress vector
\vec{t}
(Eulerian),
2- then with a unit normal vector
{\vec{n}}
, define the associated Cauchy stress tensor
{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}

(satisfying
\vec{t}={\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}.{\vec{n}}
),
3- then use the virtual power and the change of variables in integrals to be back into
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
to be able to work with Lagrangian variables,
4- then introduce the first Piola–Kirchhoff (two point) tensor
{P\!\!\!K}
,
5- then introduce the second Piola–Kirchhoff tensor
{S\!\!K}
(endomorphism in
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
),
6- then differentiate
{S\!\!K}
in
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
(in the Lagrangian variables although the initials variables are the Eulerian variables in
{\Omega_{t}}
),
7- then back in
{\Omega_{t}}
to get back to Eulerian functions (change of variables in integrals),
8- then you get some Jaumann or Truesdell or other so called Lie derivatives type terms, the appropriate choice among all these derivatives being quite obscure because the covariant objectivity has been forgotten en route… While, with simple Eulerian considerations, it requires a few lines
to understand the (real) Lie derivative (Eulerian concept) and its simplicity, see §
9
, and deduce second order covariant objective results.

.

3.5
Lagrangian acceleration

Let
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}\in{\it O\!b\!j}
,
{t_{0}},t\in\mathbb{R}
,
{p_{t_{0}}}={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}({t_{0}})
and
{p_{t}}={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}(t)
(positions of
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
at
{t_{0}}
and
t
).

Definition 3.13

In short, the Lagrangian acceleration at
t
at
{p_{t}}
of the particle
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
is

{\vec{\Gamma}^{\,t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}):={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}{}^{\prime\prime}(t)\quad\hbox{when}\quad{p_{t_{0}}}={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}({t_{0}}).

(3.36)

In other words

{\vec{\Gamma}^{\,t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}):={\vec{\gamma}}(t,{p_{t}})\quad\hbox{when}\quad{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}),

(3.37)

where
{\vec{\gamma}}(t,{p_{t}})={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}{}^{\prime\prime}(t)
is the Eulerian acceleration at
t
at
{p_{t}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
, cf. (
2.37
).

In details, the Lagrangian acceleration is the “two point vector field” defined on
\mathbb{R}\times{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
by

\widetilde{\vec{\Gamma}^{\,t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})=((t,{p_{t}}),{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}{}^{\prime\prime}(t)),\quad\hbox{when}\quad{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}).

(3.38)

(To compare with (
2.38
).) In particular
{\vec{\Gamma}^{\,t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})
is not drawn on the graph of
{\vec{\Gamma}^{\,t_{0}}}
at
(t,{p_{t_{0}}})
,
but on the graph of
{\vec{\gamma}}
at
(t,{p_{t}})
.

If
t
is fixed, or if
{p_{t_{0}}}\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
is fixed, then define

{\vec{\Gamma}_{t}^{\,t_{0}}}({p_{t_{0}}}):={\vec{\Gamma}^{\,t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}),\quad\hbox{and}\quad{\vec{\Gamma}}_{p_{t_{0}}}^{t_{0}}(t):={\vec{\Gamma}^{\,t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}).

(3.39)

Thus

{\vec{\Gamma}_{t}^{\,t_{0}}}={\vec{\gamma}}_{t}\circ{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}},\quad\hbox{and}\quad d{\vec{\Gamma}^{\,t_{0}}}_{t}({p_{t_{0}}})=d{\vec{\gamma}}_{t}({p_{t}}).{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}),

(3.40)

when
{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})
and
{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}:=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
(the deformation gradient).

Risky notation:
d{\vec{\Gamma}}=d{\vec{\gamma}}.F
(points? times?).

3.6
Time Taylor expansion of
{\Phi^{t_{0}}}

Let
{p_{t_{0}}}\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
. Then, at second order,

\displaystyle{{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(\tau)=}

\displaystyle{{}{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t)+(\tau{-}t){\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}^{\prime}(t)+{(\tau{-}t)^{2}\over 2}{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}^{\prime\prime}(t)+o((\tau{-}t)^{2}),}

(3.41)

that is, with
p(\tau)={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}(\tau)=\Phi^{t_{0}}_{\tau}({p_{t_{0}}})
,

\displaystyle{p(\tau)=}

\displaystyle{{}p(t)+(\tau{-}t){{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})+{(\tau{-}t)^{2}\over 2}{\vec{\Gamma}^{\,t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})+o((\tau{-}t)^{2}).}

(3.42)

NB: There are
three
times involved:
{t_{0}}
(observer dependent),
t
and
\tau
(for the Taylor expansion).
To compare with (
2.41
)-(
2.42
):

p(\tau)=p(t)+(\tau{-}t){\vec{v}}(t,p(t))+{(\tau{-}t)^{2}\over 2}{\vec{\gamma}}(t,p(t))+o((\tau{-}t)^{2})
, independent of
{t_{0}}
.

3.7
A vector field that let itself be deformed by a motion

Consider a
C^{0}
Eulerian vector field
{\vec{w}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\hbox{\large$\cal C$}&\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\cr(t,{p_{t}})&\rightarrow{\vec{w}}(t,{p_{t}})\cr}}\,\right\}
.
Let
{t_{0}}\in[t_{1},t_{2}[
and let

{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\Omega_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\cr{p_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}}):={\vec{w}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})\cr}}\,\right\}

(vector field in
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
).
Then define the (virtual) vector field

{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\hbox{\large$\cal C$}&\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\cr(t,{p_{t}})&\rightarrow{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}(t,{p_{t}}):=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}}),\quad\hbox{when}\quad p(t)={\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}).\cr}}\,\right.

(3.43)

(The push-forward = result of the deformation of
{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}
by the motion, see figure
4.1
.)

Proposition 3.14

For
C^{2}
motions, we have (time variation rate along a virtual trajectory)

{D{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}\over Dt}=d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*},

(3.44)

i.e.
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}=\vec{0}
,
where
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{u}}:={D{\vec{u}}\over Dt}-d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{u}}
(
={\partial{\vec{u}}\over\partial t}+d{\vec{u}}.{\vec{v}}-d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{u}}
) is the Lie derivative of a (unsteady) vector field
{\vec{u}}:\hbox{\large$\cal C$}\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
along
{\vec{v}}
.

Interpretation:
We will see that

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})=\lim_{t\rightarrow{t_{0}}}{{\vec{w}}(t,p(t))-{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}(t,p(t))\over h}
measures the “resistance of
{\vec{w}}
to a motion”, see §
9.3.2
;
Thus the result
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})=\vec{0}
is “obvious” (
=\lim_{t\rightarrow{t_{0}}}{{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}(t,p(t))-{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}(t,p(t))\over h}
):
If
