{\vec{w}}={\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}
then the vector (“force”) field
{\vec{w}}
does not oppose any resistance to the flow.

Proof.
{p_{t_{0}}}
being fixed, with
d{\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}F(t)
we have

{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}(t,p(t))\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdwdt05})}F(t).{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}})
, thus

{D{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}\over Dt}(t,p(t))=F^{\prime}(t).{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}})=F^{\prime}(t).F(t)^{-1}.{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}(t,p(t))\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqvVvv2b})}d{\vec{v}}(t,p(t)).{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}(t,p(t))
,
i.e. (
3.44
).

4
Deformation gradient
F:=d\Phi

Consider a motion

{\widetilde{\Phi}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\mathbb{R}\times{\it O\!b\!j}&\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\cr(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})&\rightarrow{p_{t}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})\crcr}}\,\right\}
,
{\Omega_{t}}:={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{\it O\!b\!j})
the configuration of
{\it O\!b\!j}
at any
t
,
fix
{t_{0}},t
in
\mathbb{R}
, and let

{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\Omega_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow{\Omega_{t}}\cr{p_{t_{0}}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}({t_{0}},{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})&\rightarrow{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}):={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})\cr}}\,\right\}
,
supposed to be a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism.
Notations for calculations (quantification), to comply with practices:

1- Classical (unambiguous) notations as in Arnold, Germain: E.g.,
({\vec{a}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
are bases resp. in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
and
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
,

{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}})=\sum_{i}w_{{t_{0}},i}({p_{t_{0}}}){\vec{a}}_{i}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
,
{\vec{w}}_{t,i}({p_{t}})=\sum_{i}w_{t,i}({p_{t}}){\vec{b}}_{i}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
; And

2- Marsden–Hughes duality notations: Capital letters at
{t_{0}}
, lower case letters at
t
, duality notation, e.g.
({\vec{E}}_{I})
and
({\vec{e}}_{i})
are bases resp. in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
and
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
,

{\vec{W}}(P)=\sum_{I}W^{I}(P){\vec{E}}_{I}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
,
{\vec{w}}(p)=\sum_{i}w^{i}(p){\vec{e}}_{i}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
.

4.1
Definitions

4.1.1
Definition of the deformation gradient
F

Definition 4.1

The differential
d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\Omega_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})\cr{p_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}):=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})\crcr}}\,\right\}

is called “the covariant deformation gradient between
{t_{0}}
and
t
”, or simply “the deformation gradient”.
And “the covariant deformation gradient at
{p_{t_{0}}}
between
{t_{0}}
and
t
”, or in short “the deformation gradient at
{p_{t_{0}}}
” is the linear map
{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})
, so defined by, for all
{{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}})}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
(vector at
{p_{t_{0}}}
),

\framebox{$\displaystyle{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).{{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}})}:=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}{+}h{{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}})})-{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})\over h}$}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})_{*}({{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}})({p_{t}}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}(t,{p_{t}}),

(4.1)

with
{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})
. See figure
4.1
.

Marsden–Hughes notations:

\Phi:={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
,
F:=d\Phi
,
P:={p_{t_{0}}}
,
{\vec{W}}(P):={{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}})}
,
p=\Phi(P)
, thus

\framebox{$\displaystyle F(P).{\vec{W}}(P):=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{\Phi(P{+}h{\vec{W}}(P))-\Phi(P)\over h}$}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\Phi_{*}{\vec{W}}(p){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{w}}_{*}(p).

(4.2)

Figure 4.1:
{\vec{w}}
is a Eulerian vector field. At
{t_{0}}
define vector field
{{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}}
in
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
by
{{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}}({p_{t_{0}}}):={\vec{w}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})
.
The (spatial) curve
c_{t_{0}}:s\rightarrow{p_{t_{0}}}=c_{t_{0}}(s)
in
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
is an integral curve of
{{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}}
, i.e. satisfies
c_{t_{0}}{}^{\prime}(s)={{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}}(c_{t_{0}}(s))
. It is transformed by
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
into the (spatial) curve
c_{t}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}\circ c_{t_{0}}:s\rightarrow{p_{t}}=c_{t}(s){=}{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(c_{t_{0}}(s))
in
{\Omega_{t}}
;
Hence
c_{t}{}^{\prime}(s)=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).c_{t_{0}}{}^{\prime}(s)=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).{{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}}({p_{t_{0}}}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}(t,{p_{t}})
is the tangent vector at
c_{t}
at
{p_{t}}
(the push-forward of
{{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}}
by
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
). And
{\vec{w}}(t,p(t))
(actual value) is also drawn.

NB:
The “deformation gradient”
{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
is
not
a “gradient” (its definition does
not
need a Euclidean dot product);
This lead to confusions when covariance-contravariance and objectivity are at stake.
It would be simpler to stick to the name “
{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}=
the differential of
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
”, but it is not the standard usage, except in thermodynamics: E.g., the differential
dU
of the internal energy
U
is
not
called “the gradient of
U
” (there is no meaningful inner dot product): It is just called “the differential of
U
”…

4.1.2
Push-forward (values of
F
)

Definition 4.2

Let
{{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\Omega_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}\cr{p_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow{{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}})}\crcr}}\,\right\}
be a vector field in
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
.
Its push-forward by
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
is the vector field

({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})_{*}({{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}})
in
{\Omega_{t}}
defined by

({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})_{*}{{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}}({p_{t}})={F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).{{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}})}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}(t,{p_{t}})\quad\hbox{when}\quad{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).

(4.3)

See figure
4.1
.
Marsden notation:
\Phi_{*}{\vec{W}}(p)=F(P).{\vec{W}}(P){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{w}}_{*}(p)
when
p={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
.

In other words

({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})_{*}{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}:=({F^{t_{0}}_{t}}.{{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}})\circ({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{-1}.

(4.4)

Marsden notation:
\Phi_{*}{\vec{W}}=(F.{\vec{W}})\circ\Phi^{-1}={\vec{w}}_{*}
.

4.1.3
F
is a two point tensors

With (
4.1
), “the tangent map” is

\widehat{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\Omega_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow{\Omega_{t}}\times{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})\cr{p_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow\widehat{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})=({p_{t}},{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}))\quad\hbox{when}\quad{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).\crcr}}\,\right.

(4.5)

Definition 4.3

(Marsden–Hughes
[
14
]
.) The function
\widehat{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}
is called a two point tensor, referring to the points
{p_{t_{0}}}\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
(departure set)
and
{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})\in{\Omega_{t}}
(arrival set where
{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}(t,{p_{t}})={F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).{{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}}({p_{t_{0}}})
is drawn).
And in short
\widehat{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}
is said to be a two point tensor.

Remark 4.4

The name “two point tensor” is a shortcut than can create confusions and errors when dealing with the transposed:
{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}
is not immediately a “tensor”:
A tensor is a multilinear form, so gives scalar results (
\in\mathbb{R}
),
while
F(P):={F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}F_{P}\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})
gives vector results (in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
).
However
F_{P}
can be naturally and canonically associated with the bilinear form
\widetilde{F}_{P}\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n*}_{t}},{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};\mathbb{R})
defined by,
for all
{\vec{u}}_{P}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
and
\ell_{p}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n*}_{t}}
, with
p={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
,

\widetilde{F}_{P}(\ell_{p},{\vec{u}}_{P}):=\ell_{p}.F_{P}.{\vec{u}}_{P}\;(\in\mathbb{R}),

(4.6)

see §
A.13
,
and it is
\widetilde{F}_{P}
which defines the so-called “two point tensor”.

But don’t forget that the transposed of a linear form (
F_{P}
here) is
not
deduced from the transposed of the associated bilinear form (
\widetilde{F}_{P}
here). So be careful with the word “transposed” and its two distinct definitions.
Indeed, the transposed of a bilinear form
b(\cdot,\cdot)
is intrinsic to
b(\cdot,\cdot)
(is objective),
given by
b^{T}({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})=b({\vec{w}},{\vec{u}})
, while the transposed of a linear function
L
is not intrinsic to
L
(is subjective),
given by
(L^{T}.{\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})_{g}=(L.{\vec{w}},{\vec{u}})_{h}
where
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
are inner dot products chosen by human beings.
(details in §
A.7.2
and §
A.11.1
).

Remark 4.5

More generally for manifolds, the differential of
\Phi:={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
at
P\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
is

F(P):=d\Phi(P):\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr T_{P}{\Omega_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow T_{p}{\Omega_{t}}\cr{\vec{W}}(P)&\rightarrow{\vec{w}}_{*}(p):=d\Phi(P).{\vec{W}}(P)\crcr}}\,\right\}
with
p={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
.
And the tangent map is

T\Phi:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr T{\Omega_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow T{\Omega_{t}}\cr(P,{\vec{W}}(P))&\rightarrow T\Phi(P,{\vec{W}}(P)):=(p,d\Phi(P).{\vec{W}}(P))=(p,{\vec{w}}_{*}(p)),\quad\hbox{where}\quad p={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P),\crcr}}\,\right.

(4.7)

called the associated two point tensor.

4.1.4
Evolution: Toward the Lie derivative (in continuum mechanics)

Consider a Eulerian vector field

{\vec{w}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\hbox{\large$\cal C$}=\bigcup_{t}(\{t\}\times{\Omega_{t}})&\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\cr(t,p)&\rightarrow{\vec{w}}(t,p)\crcr}}\,\right\}
, e.g. a “force field”.
Then, at
{t_{0}}
consider

{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\Omega_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}\cr{p_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}}):={\vec{w}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})\crcr}}\,\right\}
.
The push-forward of
{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}
by
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
is, cf. (
4.2
),

{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}(t,p(t))={F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}}),\quad\hbox{where}\quad p(t)={\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}).

(4.8)

See figure
4.1
.
Then, without any ubiquity gift, at
t
at
p(t)
we can compare
{\vec{w}}(t,p(t))
(real value of
{\vec{w}}
at
t
at
p(t)
) with
{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}(t,p(t))
(transported memory along the trajectory). Thus the rate

{{\vec{w}}(t,p(t))-{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}(t,p(t))\over t-{t_{0}}}={\hbox{\footnotesize actual}(t,p(t))-\hbox{\footnotesize memory}(t,p(t))\over t-{t_{0}}}\quad\hbox{is meaningful at $(t,p(t))$}

(4.9)

(no ubiquity gift required).
This rate gives, as
h\rightarrow 0
, the Lie derivative
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}
(the rate of stress), and we will see at §
9.3

that
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}={D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}-d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}

(the
d{\vec{v}}
term tells that a “non-uniform flow” acts on the stress).

4.1.5
Pull-back

Formally the pull-back is the push-forward with
({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{-1}
:

Definition 4.6

The pull-back
({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{*}{\vec{w}}_{t}
of a vector field
{\vec{w}}_{t}
defined on
{\Omega_{t}}
is the vector field defined on
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
by, with
{p_{t_{0}}}=({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{-1}({p_{t}})
,

{\vec{w}}_{t}^{*}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})=({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{*}{\vec{w}}_{t}({p_{t_{0}}}):=({F^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{-1}({p_{t}}).{\vec{w}}_{t}({p_{t}}),\quad\hbox{written}\quad{\vec{W}}^{*}(P)=F^{-1}(p).{\vec{w}}(p).

(4.10)

4.2
Quantification with bases

(Simple Cartesian framework.)

({\vec{a}}_{i})
is a Cartesian basis in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t_{0}}}}
,
({\vec{b}}_{i})
is a Cartesian basis in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t}}}
,
o_{t}
is an origin in
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
at
t
,
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\Phi
supposed
C^{1}
,
\varphi_{i}:{\Omega_{t_{0}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is its components in the referential
(o_{t},({\vec{b}}_{i}))
:

\Phi({p_{t_{0}}})=o_{t}+{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\varphi_{i}({p_{t_{0}}}){\vec{b}}_{i},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad\overrightarrow{o_{t}\Phi({p_{t_{0}}})}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\varphi_{i}({p_{t_{0}}}){\vec{b}}_{i}.

(4.11)

Thus, with the classic notation
d\varphi_{i}({p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{a}}_{j}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\partial\varphi_{i}\over\partial X_{j}}({p_{t_{0}}})
since
({\vec{a}}_{i})
is a Cartesian basis, and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
being a Cartesian basis,

d\Phi({p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{a}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}(d\varphi_{i}({p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{a}}_{j}){\vec{b}}_{i}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\partial\varphi_{i}\over\partial X_{j}}({p_{t_{0}}}){\vec{b}}_{i},\quad\hbox{thus}\quad[d\Phi({p_{t_{0}}})]_{[{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}]}=[{\partial\varphi_{i}\over\partial X_{j}}({p_{t_{0}}})]=[F({p_{t_{0}}})]_{[{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}]},

[d\Phi({p_{t_{0}}})]_{[{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}]}=[F({p_{t_{0}}})]_{[{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}]}
being the Jacobian matrix of
\Phi
at
{p_{t_{0}}}
relative to the chosen bases.
In short:

d\Phi.{\vec{a}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\partial\varphi_{i}\over\partial X_{j}}{\vec{b}}_{i},\quad\hbox{thus}\quad[d\Phi]_{[{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}]}=[{\partial\varphi_{i}\over\partial X_{j}}]=[F]_{[{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}]}=[F_{ij}],

(4.12)

Thus, if
{\vec{W}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
is a vector at
{p_{t_{0}}}
and
{\vec{W}}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}W_{j}{\vec{a}}_{j}
then, by linearity of differentials,

d\Phi.{\vec{W}}=F.{\vec{W}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}F_{ij}W_{j}{\vec{b}}_{i},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad[F.{\vec{W}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}=[F]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}.[{\vec{W}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}

(4.13)

(more precisely:
{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{W}}({p_{t_{0}}})={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}F_{ij}({p_{t_{0}}})W_{j}({p_{t_{0}}}){\vec{b}}_{i}
).

Similarly, for the second order derivative
d^{2}\Phi=dF
(when
\Phi
is
C^{2}
):
With
{\vec{U}}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}U_{j}{\vec{a}}_{j}
and
{\vec{W}}={\sum_{k=1}^{n}}W_{k}{\vec{a}}_{k}
, and with
({\vec{a}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
Cartesian bases, we get

dF({\vec{U}},{\vec{W}})=d^{2}\Phi({\vec{U}},{\vec{W}})={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}d^{2}\varphi_{i}({\vec{U}},{\vec{W}}){\vec{b}}_{i}={\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}}{\partial^{2}\varphi_{i}\over\partial X_{j}\partial X_{k}}U_{j}W_{k}{\vec{b}}_{i}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\;\Bigl([{\vec{U}}]^{T}_{|{\vec{a}}}.[d^{2}\varphi_{i}]_{|{\vec{a}}}.[{\vec{W}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}\Bigr)\;{\vec{b}}_{i},

(4.14)

[d^{2}\varphi_{i}({p_{t_{0}}})]_{|{\vec{a}}}=[{\partial^{2}\varphi_{i}\over\partial X_{j}\partial X_{k}}({p_{t_{0}}})]_{j=1,...,n\atop k=1,...,n}
being the Hessian matrix of
\varphi_{i}
at
{p_{t_{0}}}
relative to the basis
({\vec{a}}_{i})
.

With Marsden duality notations:

\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\bullet&p=\Phi(P)=o_{t}+{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\varphi^{i}(P){\vec{e}}_{i},\quad{F^{i}_{\;J}}(P)={\partial\varphi^{i}\over\partial X^{J}}(P)\quad(=d\varphi^{i}(P).{\vec{E}}_{J}),\cr\bullet&F(P).{\vec{W}}={\sum_{i,J=1}^{n}}{F^{i}_{\;J}}(P)\,W^{J}{\vec{e}}_{i},\quad[F]=[{F^{i}_{\;J}}]=[d\Phi],\cr\bullet&dF({\vec{U}},{\vec{W}})=d^{2}\Phi({\vec{U}},{\vec{W}})=\sum_{i,J,K=1}^{n}{\partial^{2}\varphi^{i}\over\partial X^{J}\partial X^{K}}U^{J}W^{K}{\vec{e}}_{i}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\;\Bigl([{\vec{U}}]^{T}.[d^{2}\varphi^{i}].[{\vec{W}}]\Bigr)\;{\vec{e}}_{i}.\crcr}}\,.

(4.15)

Remark 4.7

J,j
are dummy variables when used in a summation: E.g.,

df.{\vec{W}}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}{\partial f\over\partial X^{j}}W^{j}={\sum_{J=1}^{n}}{\partial f\over\partial X^{J}}W^{J}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}{\partial f\over\partial X^{\alpha}}W^{\alpha}={\partial f\over\partial X^{1}}W^{1}+{\partial f\over\partial X^{2}}W^{2}+...

(there is no uppercase for 1, 2…). And Marsden–Hughes notations (capital letters for the past) are not at all compulsory, classical notations being just as good and even preferable if you hesitate (because they are not misleading). See §
A
.

4.3
The unfortunate notation
d\vec{x}=F.d\vec{X}

4.3.1
Issue

(
4.3
), i.e.
{\vec{w}}_{*}(p):=F(P).{\vec{W}}(P)
, is sometimes written

d{\vec{x}}=F.d{\vec{X}}\hbox{ : ``a very unfortunate and misleading notation''}

(4.16)

which amounts to “confuse a length and a speed”… And you also the phrase “(
4.16
) is still true if
||d{\vec{X}}||=1
”… while
d{\vec{X}}
is supposed to be small…

4.3.2
Where does this unfortunate notation come from?

The notation (
4.16
) comes from the first order Taylor expansion
\Phi(Q)=\Phi(P)+d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P).(Q{-}P)+o(||Q{-}P||)
, where
P,Q\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
, i.e., with
p={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
and
q={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(Q)
and
h=||Q{-}P||
,

q-p=F(P).(Q{-}P)+o(h),\quad\hbox{written}\quad\delta{\vec{x}}=F.\delta{\vec{X}}+o(\delta{\vec{X}}),

(4.17)

or
\overrightarrow{pq}=F(P).\overrightarrow{PQ}+o(h)
.
So as
Q\rightarrow P
we get
0=0
… Quite useless, isn’t it?

While

{q-p\over h}=F(P).{Q-P\over h}+o(1)\quad\hbox{is useful:}

(4.18)

As
Q\rightarrow P
we get
{\vec{w}}_{*}=F(P).{\vec{W}}
which relates tangent vectors, see figure
4.1

Details:

4.3.3
Interpretation: Vector approach

Consider a spatial curve

c_{t_{0}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr[s_{1},s_{2}]&\rightarrow{\Omega_{t_{0}}}\cr s&\rightarrow P:=c_{t_{0}}(s)\crcr}}\,\right\}

in
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
, cf. figure
4.1
.
It is deformed by
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
to become the spatial curve defined by

c_{t}:={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}\circ c_{t_{0}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr[s_{1},s_{2}]&\rightarrow{\Omega_{t}}\cr s&\rightarrow p:=c_{t}(s)={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(c_{t_{0}}(s)).\crcr}}\,\right.

in
{\Omega_{t}}
.
Hence, relation between tangent vectors:

{dc_{t}\over ds}(s)=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(c_{t_{0}}(s)).{dc_{t_{0}}\over ds}(s),,\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad{\vec{w}}_{*}(p)=F(P).{\vec{W}}(P)\quad\hbox{written}\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle{d{\vec{x}}\over ds}=F.{d{\vec{X}}\over ds}$},

(4.19)

But you
can’t
simplify by
ds
to get
d{\vec{x}}=F.d{\vec{X}}
: It is absurd to confuse “a slope
{d{\vec{X}}\over ds}(s)
” and “a length
\delta{\vec{X}}
”.

NB:
||{dc_{t}\over ds}(s)||=||{d{\vec{X}}\over ds}(s)||=1
is meaningful in (
4.19
):
It means that the parametrization of the curve
c_{t_{0}}
in
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}

uses a spatial parameter
s
such that
||c_{t_{0}}{}^{\prime}(s)||=1
for all
s
,
i.e. s.t.
||{\vec{W}}_{P}||=1
in figure
4.1
.
You
cannot
simplify by
ds
:
||d{\vec{X}}||=1
is absurd together with
d{\vec{X}}
“small”.

4.3.4
Interpretation: Differential approach

(
4.16
) is a relation between differentials… if you adopt the correct notations; Let us do it: With (
4.11
),

{\vec{x}}=\overrightarrow{o_{t}p}=\overrightarrow{o_{t}{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\varphi_{i}(P){\vec{b}}_{i}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x_{i}(P){\vec{b}}_{i},\quad\hbox{where}\quad\varphi_{i}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}x_{i}\quad(\hbox{function of $P$}).

(4.20)

Thus, with
({\pi_{ai}})=(dX_{i})
the (covariant) dual basis of
({\vec{a}}_{i})
we get the system of
n
equations (functions):

d\Phi=F,\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&\textstyle d\varphi_{1}(P)={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}{\partial\varphi_{1}\over\partial X_{j}}(P)\;dX_{j}\cr&\vdots\cr&\textstyle d\varphi_{n}(P)={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}{\partial\varphi_{n}\over\partial X_{j}}(P)\;dX_{j}\cr}}\,\right\},\quad\hbox{which is noted}\quad d{\vec{x}}=F.d{\vec{X}},

(4.21)

this last notation being often misunderstood
2
2

2

Spivak
[
19
]
chapter 4:
Classical differential geometers (and classical analysts) did not hesitate to talk
about “infinitely small” changes
dx^{i}
of the coordinates
x^{i}
, just as Leibnitz had.
No one wanted to admit that this was nonsense, because true results were
obtained when these infinitely small quantities were divided into each other
(provided one did it in the right way).
Eventually it was realized that the closest one can come to describing an
infinitely small change is to describe a direction in which this change is supposed
to occur, i.e., a tangent vector. Since
df
is supposed to be the infinitesimal
change of
f
under an infinitesimal change of the point,
df
must be a function
of this change, which means that
df
should be a function on tangent vectors.
The
dX_{i}
themselves then metamorphosed into functions, and it became clear
that they must be distinguished from the tangent vectors
\partial/\partial X_{i}
.
Once this realization came, it was only a matter of making new definitions,
which preserved the old notation, and waiting for everybody to catch up.

: It is nothing more than
d\Phi=F
(coordinate free notation).

4.3.5
The ambiguous notation
\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptd\vec{x}\,}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}=\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptF}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}.d\vec{X}

The bad notation
d{\vec{x}}=F.d{\vec{X}}
gives the unfortunate and misunderstood notations

\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptd{\vec{x}}}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}=\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptF}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}.d{\vec{X}}
, and then

\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptd{\vec{x}}}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}=L.d{\vec{x}}\quad\mathrel{\hbox{where}}\quad\mathrel{L}=\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptF}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}.F^{-1}.

(4.22)

Question: What is the meaning (and legitimate notation) of (
4.22
)?

Answer:
\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptd{\vec{x}}}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}=L.d{\vec{x}}
means

\framebox{$\displaystyle{D{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}\over Dt}=d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}$}\;\;=\hbox{evolution rate of tangent vectors along a trajectory}

(4.23)

see figure
4.1
.
Indeed,
{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}(t,p(t))\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqrapvEivei2a})}}{F^{t_{0}}}(t,p_{t_{0}}).{{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}}({p_{t_{0}}})
gives

{D{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}\over Dt}(t,p(t))={\partial{F^{t_{0}}}\over\partial t}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}).{{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}}({p_{t_{0}}})={\partial{F^{t_{0}}}\over\partial t}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}).({F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})^{-1}.{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}(t,p(t))),

(4.24)

i.e.
{D{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}\over Dt}(t,p(t))=d{\vec{v}}(t,p(t)).{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}(t,p(t))
, i.e. (
4.23
).
In particular
{D{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}\over Dt}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})=d{\vec{v}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}}).{{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}}({p_{t_{0}}})
is the evolution rate of tangent vectors at
{t_{0}}
at
{p_{t_{0}}}
.

4.4
Tensorial notations, warnings, remarks

As already noted, cf. (
4.6
), the linear map
F:=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})
is naturally canonically associated with the bipoint tensor
\widetilde{F}\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n*}_{t}},{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};\mathbb{R})
defined by, for all
(\ell,{\vec{W}})\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n*}_{t}}\times{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
,

\widetilde{F}(\ell,{\vec{W}}):=\ell.F.{\vec{W}},

(4.25)

Quantification of
\widetilde{F}
: basis
({\vec{a}}_{i})
with dual basis
(\pi_{ai})
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
, basis
({\vec{b}}_{i})
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
:

\hbox{if}\quad F.{\vec{a}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\partial\varphi^{i}\over\partial X_{j}}{\vec{b}}_{j}\quad\hbox{then}\quad\widetilde{F}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}{\partial\varphi^{i}\over\partial X_{j}}\,{\vec{b}}_{i}\otimes\pi_{aj}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\vec{b}}_{i}\otimes d\varphi_{i}.

(4.26)

And similarly

d\widetilde{F}={\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}}{\partial^{2}\varphi^{i}\over\partial X_{j}\partial X_{k}}\;{\vec{b}}_{i}\otimes(\pi_{aj}\otimes\pi_{ak})={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\vec{b}}_{i}\otimes d^{2}\varphi_{i}.

(4.27)

Warning:

The tensorial notation can be misleading, in particular if you use the transposed, see remark
4.4
.
So, you should always use the standard notation for the linear form
F\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})
to begin with, i.e. use

F.{\vec{a}}_{j}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}F_{ij}{\vec{b}}_{i}
or
F.{\vec{E}}_{J}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}{F^{i}_{\;J}}{\vec{e}}_{i}
(Marsden notations).
And only use the tensorial notations for calculations purposes at the end (after application of the proper definitions).

Remark 4.8

In some manuscripts you find the notation
F=d\Phi{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\Phi\otimes\nabla_{X}
.
It does not help to understand what
F
is (it is the differential
d\Phi
),
and should not be used as far as objectivity is concerned:

\bullet
A differentiation is
not
a tensorial operation, see example
R.1
, so why use the tensor product notation
\Phi\otimes\nabla_{X}
, when the standard notation
d\Phi\simeq\widetilde{F}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes d\varphi^{i}
is legitimate, explicit, objective and easy to manipulate?

\bullet
And it could be misinterpreted, since, in mechanics,
\nabla f
is often understood to be
the vector
\sum_{i}{\partial f\over\partial x_{i}}{\vec{e}}_{i}
(contravariant) which needs a Euclidean dot product to be defined (which one?),
while the differential
df
is covariant (a differential is unmissable in thermodynamics because you can’t use gradients).

\bullet

It gives the confusing notation
\Phi\otimes\nabla_{X}\otimes\nabla_{X}
, instead of the legitimate
d^{2}\Phi={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\vec{b}}_{i}\otimes d^{2}\varphi_{i}
which is explicit, objective and easy to manipulate:

d^{2}\Phi({\vec{U}},{\vec{W}})={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}d^{2}\varphi_{i}({\vec{U}},{\vec{W}})\,{\vec{b}}_{i}
.

Exercice 4.9

Use Marsden duality notations for (
4.26
)-(
4.27
).

Answer
. Cartesian bases, with
(dX^{i})
the (covariant) dual basis of
({\vec{E}}_{i})
: with

{F^{i}_{\;J}}={\partial\varphi^{i}\over\partial X^{J}}
, we get

d\Phi{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\widetilde{F}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes d\varphi^{i}={\sum_{i,J=1}^{n}}{F^{i}_{\;J}}\,{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes dX^{J}
,
and
d^{2}\Phi={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes d^{2}\varphi^{i}=\sum_{i,J,K=1}^{n}{\partial^{2}\varphi^{i}\over\partial X^{J}\partial X^{K}}\;{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes(dX^{J}\otimes dX^{K})
.

4.5
Change of coordinate system at
t
for
F

Let
{p_{t_{0}}}\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
,
{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})\in{\Omega_{t}}
,

{\vec{W}}({p_{t_{0}}})\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
,
{\vec{w}}({p_{t}})={F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{W}}({p_{t_{0}}})\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
(its push-forward), written

{\vec{w}}=F.{\vec{W}}
for short. The observer at
{t_{0}}
used a basis
({\vec{a}}_{i})
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
.
At
t
, in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
, a first observer chooses a Cartesian basis
({{\vec{b}}_{old,i}})
,
and a second observer chooses a Cartesian basis
({{\vec{b}}_{new,i}})
.
Let
P=[P_{ij}]
be the transition matrix from
({{\vec{b}}_{old,i}})
to
({{\vec{b}}_{new,i}})
, i.e.
{{\vec{b}}_{new,j}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}P_{ij}{{\vec{b}}_{old,i}}
for all
j
.
The change of basis formula for vectors from
({{\vec{b}}_{old,i}})
to
({{\vec{b}}_{new,i}})
(in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
) gives

[{\vec{w}}]_{|{{\vec{b}}_{new}}}=P^{-1}.[{\vec{w}}]_{|{{\vec{b}}_{old}}},\quad\hbox{thus}\quad[F.{\vec{W}}]_{|{{\vec{b}}_{new}}}=P^{-1}.[F.{\vec{W}}]_{|{{\vec{b}}_{old}}}.

(4.28)

Thus

[F]_{|{\vec{a}},{{\vec{b}}_{new}}}.[{\vec{W}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}=P^{-1}.[F]_{|{\vec{a}},{{\vec{b}}_{old}}}.[{\vec{W}}]_{|{\vec{a}}},

(4.29)

true for all
{\vec{W}}
, thus

\framebox{$\displaystyle[F]_{|{\vec{a}},{{\vec{b}}_{new}}}=P^{-1}.[F]_{|{\vec{a}},{{\vec{b}}_{old}}}$}.

(4.30)

NB: (
4.30
) is
not
the change of basis formula
[L]_{|{new}}=P^{-1}.[L]_{|{old}}.P
for endomorphisms,
which would be nonsense since
F:={F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}):{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
is not an endomorphism; (
4.30
) is just the usual change of basis formula for vectors
{\vec{w}}
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
, cf. (
4.28
).

4.6
Spatial Taylor expansion of
\Phi
and
F

{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\Phi
is supposed to be
C^{2}
for all
{t_{0}},t
.
Let
P\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
,
d\Phi=F
, and
{\vec{W}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
vector at
P
. Then, in
{\Omega_{t}}
,

\displaystyle{\Phi(P{+}h{\vec{W}})=}

\displaystyle{{}\Phi(P)+h\,F(P).{\vec{W}}+{h^{2}\over 2}\,dF(P)({\vec{W}},{\vec{W}})+o(h).}

(4.31)

4.7
Time Taylor expansion of
F

The motion
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
is supposed to be
C^{3}
.
{t_{0}}\in\mathbb{R}
,
{\Phi^{t_{0}}}
be the associated motion,

p(t)={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
and
{p_{t_{0}}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}({t_{0}},{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
, with
{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}})={\partial{\widetilde{\Phi}}\over\partial t}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
the Eulerian velocity and
{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}):={\partial{\Phi^{t_{0}}}\over\partial t}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})={\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}})
the Lagrangian velocity, and
{F^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t)={F^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}F(t)
.
We have

\displaystyle{{\partial{F^{t_{0}}}\over\partial t}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})=}

\displaystyle{{}{\partial(d{\Phi^{t_{0}}})\over\partial t}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})=d({\partial{\Phi^{t_{0}}}\over\partial t})(t,{p_{t_{0}}})}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}d{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})=d{\vec{v}}(t,p(t)).F(t),\quad\hbox{in short}\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptF}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}=d{\vec{V}}=d{\vec{v}}.F$}.}

(4.32)

Then
{\partial^{2}{\Phi^{t_{0}}}\over\partial t^{2}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})={{\vec{A}}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})={\vec{\gamma}}(t,p(t))

(Lagrangian and Eulerian accelerations),
hence

{\partial^{2}{F^{t_{0}}}\over\partial t^{2}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})=d{{\vec{A}}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})=d{\vec{\gamma}}(t,p_{t}).F(t),\quad\hbox{in short}\quad\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptF}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet\bullet$}}}=d{\vec{A}}=d{\vec{\gamma}}.F.

(4.33)

Thus, the second order time Taylor expansion of
{F^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}F
is, in the vicinity of
t
,

\displaystyle{F(t{+}h)=}

\displaystyle{{}F(t)+h\,d{\vec{V}}(t)+{h^{2}\over 2}\,d{\vec{A}}(t)+o(h^{2})}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}\Bigl(I+h\,d{\vec{v}}+{h^{2}\over 2}\,d{\vec{\gamma}}\Bigr)(t,p(t)).F(t)+o(h^{2})\quad\hbox{when}\quad p(t)={\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}).}

(4.34)

NB: They are
three
times are involved:
t
and
t{+}h
as usual, and
{t_{0}}
through
F:={F^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}
,
{\vec{V}}:={\vec{V}^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}
and
{\vec{A}}:={\vec{A}^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}
(observer dependent), as for (
3.41
).

In particular
F({p_{t_{0}}}):=F^{t_{0}}_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}})=I
gives, in the vicinity of
{t_{0}}
,

F({t_{0}}{+}h)=\bigl(I+h\,d{\vec{v}}+{h^{2}\over 2}\,d{\vec{\gamma}}\bigr)({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})+o(h^{2}).

(4.35)

Remark 4.10

\gamma={\partial{\vec{v}}\over\partial t}+d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{v}}
is not linear in
{\vec{v}}
. Idem,

\displaystyle{d{\vec{\gamma}}=}

\displaystyle{{}d({D{\vec{v}}\over Dt})=d({\partial{\vec{v}}\over\partial t}+d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{v}})=d{\partial{\vec{v}}\over\partial t}+d^{2}{\vec{v}}.{\vec{v}}+d{\vec{v}}.d{\vec{v}}\quad(={D(d{\vec{v}})\over Dt}+d{\vec{v}}.d{\vec{v}})}

(4.36)

is non linear in
{\vec{v}}
, and gives

{F^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}^{\prime\prime}(t)=(d{\partial{\vec{v}}\over\partial t}+d^{2}{\vec{v}}.{\vec{v}}+d{\vec{v}}.d{\vec{v}})(t,p_{t}).{F^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t),

non linear in
{\vec{v}}
.

Exercice 4.11

Directly check that
F^{\prime}=d{\vec{v}}.F
gives
F^{\prime\prime}=d{\vec{\gamma}}.F
.

Answer
.
F^{\prime}(t)=d{\vec{v}}(t,p(t)).F(t)
gives

F^{\prime\prime}(t)={D(d{\vec{v}})\over Dt}(t,p(t)).F(t)+d{\vec{v}}(t,p(t)).F^{\prime}(t)

with
{D(d{\vec{v}})\over Dt}=d{\vec{\gamma}}-d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{v}}
, cf. (
4.36
),
thus

F^{\prime\prime}(t)=(d{\vec{\gamma}}-d{\vec{v}}.d{\vec{v}})(t,p(t)).F(t)+d{\vec{v}}(t,p(t)).d{\vec{v}}(t,p(t)).F(t)=d{\vec{\gamma}}(t,p(t)).F(t)
.

4.8
Homogeneous and isotropic material

Let
P\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
, let
{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P):=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
;
Suppose that the “Cauchy stress vector”
{\vec{f}}_{t}({p_{t}})

à
t
at
{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
only depends on
P
, i.e. there exists a function
\vec{\hbox{fun}}
such that

{\vec{f}}_{t}({p_{t}})=\vec{\hbox{fun}}(P,{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)).

(4.37)

Definition 4.12

A material is homogeneous iff
\vec{\hbox{fun}}
doesn’t depend on the first variable
P
,
i.e., iff, for all
P\in\Omega_{t_{0}}
,

\vec{\hbox{fun}}(P,{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P))=\vec{\hbox{fun}}({F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)).

(4.38)

(Same mechanical property at any point.)

Definition 4.13

(Isotropy.)
Consider a Euclidean dot product, the same at all time.
A material is isotropic at
P\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
iff
\vec{\hbox{fun}}

is independent of the direction you consider,
i.e., iff, for any rotation
R_{t_{0}}(P)
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
,

\vec{\hbox{fun}}(P,{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)=\vec{\hbox{fun}}(P,{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P).R_{t_{0}}(P)).

(4.39)

(Mechanical property unchanged when rotating the material first.)

Definition 4.14

A material is isotropic homogeneous iff it is isotropic and homogeneous.

4.9
The inverse of the deformation gradient

(({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{-1}\circ{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})(P)=P
gives, with
p={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
,

d({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{-1}(p).d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)=I_{t_{0}},\quad\hbox{thus}\quad d({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{-1}(p)=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)^{-1}={F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)^{-1},

(4.40)

where
{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
is the deformation gradient.
We have thus define the two point tensor

{H^{t_{0}}_{t}}:=({F^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{-1}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\Omega_{t}}&\rightarrow{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}})\cr p&\rightarrow\framebox{$\displaystyle{H^{t_{0}}_{t}}(p)=({F^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{-1}(p):=({F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P))^{-1}$}\quad\hbox{when}\quad p={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P).\crcr}}\,\right.

(4.41)

So

{H^{t_{0}}_{t}}(p).{\vec{w}}(p)=({F^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{-1}(p).{\vec{w}}(p):={F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)^{-1}.{\vec{w}}(p)\;\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}},\quad\hbox{in short}\quad H.{\vec{w}}=F^{-1}.{\vec{w}},

(4.42)

for all
{\vec{w}}(p)\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
vector at
p
. This defines, with
{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,P)
,

{H^{t_{0}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\hbox{\large$\cal C$}=\bigcup_{t}(\{t\}\times{\Omega_{t}})&\rightarrow{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}})\cr(t,{p_{t}})&\rightarrow{H^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t}}):={H^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t}})=({F^{t_{0}}}(t,P))^{-1}.\crcr}}\,\right.

(4.43)

NB:
{H^{t_{0}}}
looks like a Eulerian map, but isn’t:

{H^{t_{0}}}
depends on a initial time
{t_{0}}
and is a two point tensor (starts in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
, arrives in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
). We will however use the material time derivative
{D\over Dt}
notation in this case, that is, we define, along a trajectory
t\rightarrow p(t)={\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,P)
,

{D{H^{t_{0}}}\over Dt}(t,p(t)):={\partial{H^{t_{0}}}\over\partial t}(t,p(t))+d{H^{t_{0}}}(t,p(t)).{\vec{v}}(t,p(t)),\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad{D{H^{t_{0}}}\over Dt}={\partial{H^{t_{0}}}\over\partial t}+d{H^{t_{0}}}.{\vec{v}},

(4.44)

which is the time derivative
g^{\prime}(t)
of the function
g:t\rightarrow g(t)={H^{t_{0}}}(t,{\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,P))
(i.e.
g(t)={H^{t_{0}}}(t,p(t))
).

Hence, with
p(t)={\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,P)
and
{H^{t_{0}}}(t,p(t)).{F^{t_{0}}}(t,P)=I_{t_{0}}
, written
H.F=I
, we get

{DH\over Dt}.F+H.{\partial F\over\partial t}=0,\quad\hbox{thus}\quad{DH\over Dt}=-H.d{\vec{v}},

(4.45)

since
{\partial F\over\partial t}(t,P).F^{-1}(t,p(t))=d{\vec{v}}(t,p(t))
cf. (
4.32
).

Exercice 4.15

With
{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}(t,p(t))={F^{t_{0}}}(t,P).{\vec{W}}(P)
, i.e.
{H^{t_{0}}}(t,p(t)).{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}(t,p(t))={\vec{W}}(P)
, when
p(t)={\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,P)
,
prove (
4.45
).

Answer
.
{D{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}\over Dt}(t,p(t))=d{\vec{v}}(t,p(t)).{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}(t,p(t))
, cf. (
4.23
);
And
({H^{t_{0}}}.{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*})(t,p(t))={\vec{W}}(P)
gives

{D{H^{t_{0}}}\over Dt}.{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}+{H^{t_{0}}}.{D{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}\over Dt}=0
;
Thus
{D{H^{t_{0}}}\over Dt}.{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}+{H^{t_{0}}}.d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}=0
,
thus
{DH\over Dt}=-H.d{\vec{v}}
.

Exercice 4.16

Prove:
{H^{t_{0}}_{t}}=H^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}}\circ H^{t_{1}}_{t}
and
{D{H^{t_{0}}}\over Dt}(t,p(t))=H^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}}(p_{t_{1}}).{DH^{t_{1}}\over Dt}(t,p(t))
for all
{t_{0}},t_{1}
with
p_{t_{1}}=\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}}(p_{t_{0}})
.

Answer
. We have
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(p_{t_{0}})=\Phi^{t_{1}}_{t}(\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}}(p_{t_{0}}))
, cf. (
5.18
),
hence
{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})=F^{t_{1}}_{t}(p_{t_{1}}).F^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}}({p_{t_{0}}})
,
thus
{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})^{-1}=F^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}}({p_{t_{0}}})^{-1}.F^{t_{1}}_{t}(p_{t_{1}})^{-1}
,
i.e.
{H^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t}})=H^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}}(p_{t_{1}}).H^{t_{1}}_{t}(p(t))
,
thus,
{H^{t_{0}}}(t,p(t))=H^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}}(p_{t_{1}}).H^{t_{1}}(t,p(t))
,
thus
{D{H^{t_{0}}}\over Dt}(t,p(t))=H^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}}(p_{t_{1}}).{DH^{t_{1}}\over Dt}(t,p(t))
.

5
Flow

5.1
Introduction: Motion versus flow

\bullet

Motion: A motion
{\widetilde{\Phi}}:(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})\rightarrow{p_{t}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
locates at
t
a particle
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
in the affine space
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
, cf. (
1.5
); From which the Eulerian velocity field
{\vec{v}}
is deduced:

{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}}):={d{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}\over dt}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
, cf. (
2.4
).

\bullet
Flow: A flow starts with a Eulerian velocity field
{\vec{v}}
, from which we deduce a motion
by solving the ODE (ordinary differential equation)
{d\Phi\over dt}(t)={\vec{v}}(t,\Phi(t))
.

5.2
Definition

Let
{\vec{v}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\mathbb{R}\times{\mathbb{R}^{n}}&\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\cr(t,p)&\rightarrow{\vec{v}}(t,p)\cr}}\,\right\}
be a unstationary vector field (e.g., a Eulerian velocity field which definition domain is
\hbox{\large$\cal C$}=\bigcup_{t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]}(\{t\}\times{\Omega_{t}})
). We look for maps

\Phi:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\mathbb{R}&\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\cr t&\rightarrow p=\Phi(t)\crcr}}\,\right\}

which are locally (i.e. in the vicinity of some
{t_{0}}
) solutions of the ODE (ordinary differential equation)

{d\Phi\over dt}(t)={\vec{v}}(t,\Phi(t)),\quad\hbox{also written}\quad{dp\over dt}(t)={\vec{v}}(t,p(t)),\quad\hbox{or}\quad{d{\vec{x}}\over dt}(t)={\vec{v}}(t,{\vec{x}}(t))

(5.1)

where
{\vec{x}}(t)=\overrightarrow{{\cal O}p(t)}
after a choice of an origin. Also written
{dp\over dt}={\vec{v}}(t,p)
or
{d{\vec{x}}\over dt}={\vec{v}}(t,{\vec{x}})
.

Definition 5.1

A solution
\Phi
of (
5.1
) is a flow of
{\vec{v}}
;
Also called an integral curve of
{\vec{v}}
since (
5.1
) also reads

\Phi(t)=\int_{\tau=t_{1}}^{t}{\vec{v}}(\tau,\Phi(\tau))\,d\tau+\Phi(t_{1})
.

Remark 5.2

Improper notation for (
5.1
):

{dp\over dt}(t){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{dp(t)\over dt}\qquad(={\vec{v}}(t,p(t))).

(5.2)

Question: If the notation
{dp(t)\over dt}
is used, then what is the meaning of
{dp(f(t))\over dt}
?

Answer: It means,
either
{dp\over dt}(f(t))
,
or
{d(p\circ f)\over dt}(t)={dp\over dt}(f(t)){df\over dt}(t)
: Ambiguous.
So it is better to use
{dp\over dt}(t)
, and to avoid
{dp(t)\over dt}
,
unless the context is clear (no composite functions).

5.3
Cauchy–Lipschitz theorem

Let
({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})
be in the definition domain of
{\vec{v}}
. We look for
\Phi
solution of “the ODE with initial condition
({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})
”, in some vicinity of
{t_{0}}
, i.e. such that

{d\Phi\over dt}(t)={\vec{v}}(t,\Phi(t))\quad\hbox{and}\quad\Phi({t_{0}})={p_{t_{0}}}.

(5.3)

(The couple
({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})
is the initial condition, and the values
{t_{0}}
and
{p_{t_{0}}}
are the initial conditions.)

Definition 5.3

Let
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
,
t_{1}<t_{2}
.
Let
\Omega
be an open set in
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
and
{\overline{\Omega}}
its closure supposed to be a regular domain.
Let
||.||
be a norm in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
.
A continuous map
{\vec{v}}:[t_{1},t_{2}]\times{\overline{\Omega}}\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}

is Lipschitzian iff it is “space Lipschitzian, uniformly in time”, that is, iff

\exists k>0,\;\forall t\in[t_{1},t_{2}],\;\forall p,q\in{\overline{\Omega}},\;||{\vec{v}}(t,q)-{\vec{v}}(t,p)||\leq k||q-p||.

(5.4)

So,
{||{\vec{v}}_{t}(q)-{\vec{v}}_{t}(p)||\over||q-p||}\leq k
, for all
t
and all
p\neq q

(the variations of
{\vec{v}}
are bounded in space, uniformly in time).

Theorem 5.4
(and definifion)

(Cauchy–Lipschitz).

If
{\vec{v}}:[t_{1},t_{2}]\times{\overline{\Omega}}\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
is Lipschitzian and
({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})\in]t_{1},t_{2}[\times\Omega
, then there exists
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{{t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}}}>0
s.t. (
5.3
) has a unique solution

\Phi:]{t_{0}}{-}\varepsilon,{t_{0}}{+}\varepsilon[\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
, noted
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}
:

{d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}\over dt}(t)={\vec{v}}(t,{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t))\quad\hbox{and}\quad{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}({t_{0}})={p_{t_{0}}}.

(5.5)

Moreover, if
{\vec{v}}
is
C^{k}
then
\Phi
is
C^{k+1}
.

Proof.
See e.g. Arnold
[
4
]
, or any ODE course.
In particular

\displaystyle||{\vec{v}}||_{\infty}:=\sup_{t\in]{t_{0}}{-}\varepsilon,{t_{0}}{+}\varepsilon[,\;p\in\Omega}||{\vec{v}}(t,p)||_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}

(maximum speed) exists since
{\vec{v}}\in C^{0}
on the compact
[t_{1},t_{2}]\times{\overline{\Omega}}
),
see definition
5.3
,
hence we can choose
\varepsilon=\min({t_{0}}{-}t_{1},t_{2}{-}{t_{0}},{d({p_{t_{0}}},\partial\Omega)\over||{\vec{v}}||_{\infty}})

(the time needed to reach the border
\partial\Omega
from
{p_{t_{0}}}
).

We have thus defined the function, also called “a flow”,

\Phi:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr]t_{1},t_{2}[\times]t_{1},t_{2}[\times\,{\Omega_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow\Omega\cr(t,{t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})&\rightarrow p=\Phi(t,{t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}}):={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\Phi(t;{t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}}).\crcr}}\,\right.

(5.6)

And (
5.5
) reads

{\partial\Phi\over\partial t}(t;{t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})={\vec{v}}(t,\Phi(t;{t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})),\quad\hbox{with}\quad\Phi({t_{0}};{t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})={p_{t_{0}}}.

(5.7)

We have thus defined the function, also called “a flow”,

{\Phi^{t_{0}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr[{t_{0}}{-}\varepsilon,{t_{0}}{+}\varepsilon]\times{\Omega_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\cr(t,{p_{t_{0}}})&\rightarrow p={\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}):={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t):\cr}}\,\right.

(5.8)

And (
5.5
) reads

{\partial{\Phi^{t_{0}}}\over\partial t}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})={\vec{v}}(t,{\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})),\quad\hbox{and}\quad{\Phi^{t_{0}}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})={p_{t_{0}}}.

(5.9)

Other definition and notation (can be ambiguous):
\Phi_{t;{t_{0}}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}:{\Omega_{t_{0}}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
, and (
5.7
) is written

{d\Phi_{t;{t_{0}}}({p_{t_{0}}})\over dt}={\vec{v}}(t,\Phi_{t;{t_{0}}}({p_{t_{0}}})),\quad\hbox{and}\quad\Phi_{{t_{0}};{t_{0}}}({p_{t_{0}}})={p_{t_{0}}}.

(5.10)

Theorem 5.5

Let
{\vec{v}}
be Lipschitzian, let
{t_{0}}\in]t_{1},t_{2}[
, and let
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
be an open set s.t.
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}\subset\subset\Omega
(i.e. there exists a compact set
K\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
s.t.
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}\subset K\subset\Omega
).
Then there exists
\varepsilon>0
s.t. a flow
{\Phi^{t_{0}}}
exists on
]{t_{0}}{-}\varepsilon,{t_{0}}{+}\varepsilon[\times{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
.

Proof.
Let
d=d(K,{\mathbb{R}^{n}}{-}\Omega)
(la distance of
K
to the border of
\Omega
.

Let
\displaystyle||{\vec{v}}||_{\infty}:=\sup_{t\in[t_{1},t_{2}],p\in{\overline{\Omega}}}||{\vec{v}}(t,p)||_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}

(exists since
{\vec{v}}\in C^{0}
on the compact
[t_{1},t_{2}]\times{\overline{\Omega}}
).

Let
\varepsilon=\min({t_{0}}{-}t_{1},t_{2}{-}{t_{0}},{d\over||{\vec{v}}||_{\infty}})

(less that the minimum time to reach the border from
K
at maximum speed
||v||_{\infty}
).

Let
{p_{t_{0}}}\in K
and
t\in]{t_{0}}{-}\varepsilon,{t_{0}}{+}\varepsilon[
. Then
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}
exists, cf.theorem
5.4
,
and

||{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t)-{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}({t_{0}})||_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\leq[t-{t_{0}}|\,\sup_{\tau\in]{t_{0}}{-}\varepsilon,{t_{0}}{+}\varepsilon[}(||({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}})^{\prime}(\tau)||_{\mathbb{R}^{n}})

(mean value theorem since,
{\vec{v}}
being
C^{0}
,
\Phi
is
C^{1}
).
Thus

||{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t)-{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}({t_{0}})||_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\leq[t-{t_{0}}|\,||v||_{\infty}
,
thus
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t)\in\Omega
.
Thus
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}
exists on
]{t_{0}}{-}\varepsilon,{t_{0}}{+}\varepsilon[
, for all
{p_{t_{0}}}\in K
.

Remark 5.6

The definition of a flow starts with a Eulerian velocity (independent of any initial time),
and then, due to the introduction of initial conditions, leads to the Lagrangian functions
{\Phi^{t_{0}}}
, cf. (
5.8
).
Once again, Lagrangian functions are the result of Eulerian functions.

5.4
Examples

Example 1

\mathbb{R}^{2}
with an origin
{\cal O}
, a Euclidean basis
({\vec{e}}_{1},{\vec{e}}_{2})
and
\Omega=[0,2]\times[0,1]
(observation window).
Let
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
\overrightarrow{{\cal O}p}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{x}}=x{\vec{e}}_{1}+y{\vec{e}}_{2}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}(x,y)
.
Let
t_{1}=-1
,
t_{2}=1
,
{t_{0}}\in]t_{1},t_{2}[
,
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
,
a\neq 0
, and

{\vec{v}}(t,p)=\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&v^{1}(t,x,y)=ay,\cr&v^{2}(t,x,y)=b\sin(t{-}{t_{0}}).\cr}}\,\right.

(5.11)

(
b=0
corresponds to the stationary case = shear flow.)
{\vec{x}}({t_{0}})=\pmatrix{x_{0}\cr y_{0}}
,

{\vec{x}}(t)=\pmatrix{x(t)\cr y(t)}=\overrightarrow{{\cal O}{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t)}
and (
5.9
) give

\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&{dx\over dt}(t)=v^{1}(t,x(t),y(t))=ay(t),\cr&{dy\over dt}(t)=v^{2}(t,x(t),y(t))=b\sin(t{-}{t_{0}}),\cr}}\,\right.\quad\hbox{with}\quad\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&x({t_{0}})=x_{0},\cr&y({t_{0}})=y_{0}.\cr}}\,\right.

(5.12)

Thus

{\vec{x}}(t)=\overrightarrow{{\cal O}p(t)}=\overrightarrow{{\cal O}{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t)}=\pmatrix{x(t)=x_{0}+a(y_{0}+b)(t{-}{t_{0}})-ab\sin(t{-}{t_{0}})\cr y(t)=y_{0}+b-b\cos(t{-}{t_{0}})\hfill\cr}.

(5.13)

Example 2

Similar framework. Let
\omega>0
and consider (spin vector field)

{\vec{v}}(t,x,y)=\pmatrix{-\omega y\cr\omega x}=\omega\pmatrix{0&-1\cr 1&0}\pmatrix{x\cr y}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{v}}(x,y).

(5.14)

With
\overrightarrow{{\cal O}{p_{t_{0}}}}={\vec{x}}_{t_{0}}=\pmatrix{x_{t_{0}}\cr y_{t_{0}}}
,

r_{t_{0}}=\sqrt{x_{t_{0}}^{2}+y_{t_{0}}^{2}}
, and
\theta_{0}
s.t.
{\vec{x}}_{t_{0}}=\pmatrix{x_{t_{0}}=r_{t_{0}}\cos(\omega{t_{0}})\cr y_{t_{0}}=r_{t_{0}}\sin(\omega{t_{0}})}
,
the solution
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}
of (
5.9
) is

{\vec{x}}(t)=\overrightarrow{{\cal O}p(t)}=\overrightarrow{{\cal O}{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t)}=\pmatrix{x(t)=r_{t_{0}}\cos(\omega t)\cr y(t)=r_{t_{0}}\sin(\omega t)\hfill}.

(5.15)

Indeed,

\pmatrix{{\partial x\over\partial t}(t,{\vec{x}}_{0})\cr{\partial y\over\partial t}(t,{\vec{x}}_{0})}=\pmatrix{v^{1}(t,x(t,{\vec{x}}_{0}),y(t,{\vec{x}}_{0}))\cr v^{2}(t,x(t,{\vec{x}}_{0}),y(t,{\vec{x}}_{0}))}=\pmatrix{-\omega y(t,{\vec{x}}_{0})\cr\omega x(t,{\vec{x}}_{0})}
,
thus

{\partial x\over\partial t}(t,{\vec{x}}_{0})=-\omega y(t,{\vec{x}}_{0})

and
{\partial y\over\partial t}(t,{\vec{x}}_{0})=\omega x(t,{\vec{x}}_{0})
,
thus
{\partial^{2}y\over\partial t^{2}}(t,{\vec{x}}_{0})=-\omega^{2}y(t,{\vec{x}}_{0})
,
hence
y
; Idem for
x
.
Here
d{\vec{v}}(t,x,y)=\omega\pmatrix{0&-1\cr 1&0}=\omega\pmatrix{\cos{\pi\over 2}&-\sin{\pi\over 2}\cr\sin{\pi\over 2}&\cos{\pi\over 2}}

is the
\pi/2
-rotation composed with the homothety with ratio
\omega
.

5.5
Composition of flows

Let
{\vec{v}}
be a vector field on
\mathbb{R}\times\Omega
and
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}
solution of (
5.5
).
We use the notations

{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})=\Phi_{t;{t_{0}}}({p_{t_{0}}}):={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t)={\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})=\Phi(t;{t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})=\Phi_{{t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}}}(t).

(5.16)

5.5.1
Law of composition of flows (determinism)

Proposition 5.7

For all
t_{0},t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
, we have (determinism)

\Phi^{t_{1}}_{t_{2}}\circ\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}}=\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t_{2}},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad\Phi_{t_{2};t_{1}}\circ\Phi_{t_{1};{t_{0}}}=\Phi_{t_{2};{t_{0}}}.

(5.17)

(“The composition of the photos gives the film”).
So,

p_{t_{2}}=\Phi^{t_{1}}_{t_{2}}(p_{t_{1}})=\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t_{2}}(p_{t_{0}})\quad\hbox{when}\quad p_{t_{1}}=\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}}(p_{t_{0}}),

(5.18)

i.e.,

p_{t_{2}}=\Phi_{t_{2};t_{1}}(p_{t_{1}})=\Phi_{t_{2};{t_{0}}}(p_{t_{0}})\quad\hbox{when}\quad p_{t_{1}}=\Phi_{t_{1};{t_{0}}}(p_{t_{0}}).

(5.19)

Thus

d\Phi^{t_{1}}_{t_{2}}(p_{t_{1}}).d\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}}(p_{t_{0}})=d\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t_{2}}(p_{t_{0}}),\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad d\Phi_{t_{2};t_{1}}(p_{t_{1}}).d\Phi_{t_{1};{t_{0}}}(p_{t_{0}})=d\Phi_{t_{2};{t_{0}}}(p_{t_{0}}).

(5.20)

Summary with commutative diagrams:

\textstyle{p_{t_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textstyle\Phi^{t_{1}}_{t_{2}}}
\textstyle{p_{t_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textstyle\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\textstyle\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t_{2}}}
\textstyle{p_{t_{2}}}

i.e.

\textstyle{p_{t_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textstyle\Phi_{t_{2};t_{1}}}
\textstyle{p_{t_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textstyle\Phi_{t_{1};{t_{0}}}}
\scriptstyle{\textstyle\Phi_{t_{2};{t_{0}}}}
\textstyle{p_{t_{2}}}

Proof.
Let
p_{t_{1}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t_{1})
.
(
5.9
) gives

\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&{d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}\over dt}(t)={\vec{v}}(t,{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t)),\cr&{d\Phi^{t_{1}}_{p_{t_{1}}}\over dt}(t)={\vec{v}}(t,\Phi^{t_{1}}_{p_{t_{1}}}(t)),\crcr}}\,\right\}\quad\hbox{with}\quad p_{t_{1}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t_{1})=\Phi^{t_{1}}_{p_{t_{1}}}(t_{1}).

Thus
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}
and
\Phi^{t_{1}}_{p_{t_{1}}}
satisfy the same ODE
with the same value at
t_{1}
;
Thus they are equal (uniqueness thanks to Cauchy–Lipschitz theorem),
thus
\Phi^{t_{1}}_{p_{t_{1}}}(t)={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t)
when
p_{t_{1}}=\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}}({p_{t_{0}}})
,
that is,
\Phi^{t_{1}}_{t}(p_{t_{1}})={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})
when
p_{t_{1}}=\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}}({p_{t_{0}}})
,
which is (
5.17
) for any
t=t_{2}
. Thus (
5.20
).

Corollary 5.8

A flow is compatible with the motion
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
of an object
{\it O\!b\!j}
:
(
3.6
) gives

\Phi^{t_{1}}_{t_{2}}\circ\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}}=({\widetilde{\Phi}}_{t_{2}}\circ({\widetilde{\Phi}}_{t_{1}})^{-1})\circ({\widetilde{\Phi}}_{t_{1}}\circ({\widetilde{\Phi}}_{t_{0}})^{-1})={\widetilde{\Phi}}_{t_{2}}\circ({\widetilde{\Phi}}_{t_{0}})^{-1}=\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t_{2}}
, that is (
5.17
).

5.5.2
Stationnary case

Definition 5.9

{\vec{v}}
is a stationary vector field iff
{\partial{\vec{v}}\over\partial t}=0
;
And then
{\vec{v}}(t,p){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{v}}(p)
.
And the associated flow
{\Phi^{t_{0}}}
which satisfies

{\partial{\Phi^{t_{0}}}\over\partial t}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})={\vec{v}}({p_{t}})\quad\hbox{when}\quad{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}),

(5.21)

is said to be stationary.

Proposition 5.10

If
{\vec{v}}
is a stationary vector field then, for all
{t_{0}},t_{1},h
, when meaningful (
h
small enough and
t_{1}
close enough to
{t_{0}}
),

\Phi^{t_{1}}_{t_{1}+h}=\Phi^{t_{0}}_{{t_{0}}+h},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad\Phi_{t_{1}+h;t_{1}}=\Phi_{{t_{0}}+h;{t_{0}}},

(5.22)

i.e.
\Phi^{t_{1}}_{t_{1}+h}(q)=\Phi^{t_{0}}_{{t_{0}}+h}(q)
, i.e.
\Phi(t_{1}{+}h;t_{1},q)=\Phi({t_{0}}{+}h;{t_{0}},q)
for all
q\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
(see theorem
5.5
).
In other words,

\Phi^{{t_{0}}+h}_{t_{1}+h}=\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad\Phi_{t_{1}+h;{t_{0}}+h}=\Phi_{t_{1};{t_{0}}},

(5.23)

i.e.
\Phi^{{t_{0}}+h}_{t_{1}+h}(q)=\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}}(q)
, i.e.
\Phi(t_{1}{+}h;{t_{0}}{+}h,q)=\Phi(t_{1};{t_{0}},q)
for all
q\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
.

Proof.
Let
q\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
,
\alpha(h)=\Phi^{t_{0}}_{{t_{0}}{+}h}(q)=\Phi^{t_{0}}_{q}({t_{0}}{+}h)
and

\beta(h)=\Phi^{t_{1}}_{t_{1}{+}h}(q)=\Phi^{t_{1}}_{q}(t_{1}{+}h)
.

Thus
\alpha^{\prime}(h)={d\Phi^{t_{0}}_{q}\over dt}({t_{0}}{+}h)={\vec{v}}({t_{0}}{+}h,\Phi^{t_{0}}_{q}({t_{0}}{+}h))={\vec{v}}(\Phi^{t_{0}}_{q}({t_{0}}{+}h))={\vec{v}}(\alpha(h))
(stationary flow), and

\beta^{\prime}(h)={d\Phi{t_{1}}_{q}\over dt}(t_{1}{+}h)={\vec{v}}(t_{1}{+}h,\Phi^{t_{1}}_{q}(t_{1}{+}h))={\vec{v}}(\Phi^{t_{1}}_{q}(t_{1}{+}h))={\vec{v}}(\beta(h))
(stationary flow).

Thus
\alpha
and
\beta
satisfy the same ODE with the same initial condition
\alpha(0)=\beta(0)=q
.
Thus
\alpha=\beta
. Hence (
5.22
).
Thus, with
h=t_{1}{-}{t_{0}}
, i.e. with
t_{1}={t_{0}}{+}h
and
{t_{0}}{+}h=t_{1}
, we get (
5.23
).

Corollary 5.11

If
{\vec{v}}
is a stationary vector field, cf. (
5.21
), then

d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{v}}({p_{t_{0}}})={\vec{v}}({p_{t}})\quad\hbox{when}\quad{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}),

(5.24)

that is, if
{\vec{v}}
is stationary, then
{\vec{v}}
is transported (push-forwarded by
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
) along itself.

Proof.
(
5.18
),
t_{2}=t_{1}{+}s
and
t_{1}={t_{0}}{+}s
give

\Phi^{{t_{0}}{+}s}_{t_{1}{+}s}(\Phi^{t_{0}}_{{t_{0}}{+}s}(p_{t_{0}}))=\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}{+}s}(p_{t_{0}})
,
and
{\vec{v}}
is stationary,
thus
\Phi^{{t_{0}}}_{t_{1}}(\Phi^{t_{0}}_{{t_{0}}{+}s}(p_{t_{0}}))=\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}{+}s}(p_{t_{0}})
,
i.e.
\Phi(t_{1};{t_{0}},\Phi_{{t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}}}({t_{0}}{+}s))=\Phi_{{t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}}}(t_{1}{+}s)
, thus (
s
derivative)

d\Phi(t_{1};{t_{0}},\Phi({t_{0}}{+}s;{t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})).\Phi_{{t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}}}{}^{\prime}({t_{0}}{+}s)=\Phi_{{t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}}}{}^{\prime}(t_{1}{+}s),

thus
d\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}}(\Phi({t_{0}}{+}s;{t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})).{\vec{v}}({t_{0}}{+}s,\Phi_{{t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}}}({t_{0}}{+}s))={\vec{v}}(t_{1}{+}s,\Phi_{{t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}}}(t_{1}{+}s))
. Thus with
s=0
, and
{\vec{v}}
being stationary,

d\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}}(\Phi({t_{0}};{t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})).{\vec{v}}(\Phi_{{t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}}}(t_{0}))={\vec{v}}(\Phi_{{t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}}}(t_{1}))
, thus (
5.24
).

5.6
Velocity on the trajectory traveled in the opposite direction

Let
{t_{0}},{t_{1}}\in\mathbb{R}
,
t_{1}>{t_{0}}
, and
{p_{t_{0}}}\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
. Consider the trajectory

{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr[{t_{0}},{t_{1}}]&\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\cr t&\rightarrow p(t)={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t)\crcr}}\,\right\}
.
So
{p_{t_{0}}}
is the beginning of the trajectory,
{p_{t_{1}}}=\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}}({p_{t_{0}}})
at the end,
{\vec{v}}(t,p(t))={d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}\over dt}(t)
being the velocity.

Define the trajectory traveled in the opposite direction, i.e. define

\Psi^{t_{1}}_{p_{t_{1}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr[{t_{0}},{t_{1}}]&\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\cr u&\rightarrow q(u)=\Psi^{t_{1}}_{p_{t_{1}}}(u):={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}({t_{0}}{+}{t_{1}}{-}u)={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t)=p(t)\quad\hbox{when}\quad t={t_{0}}{+}{t_{1}}{-}u.\crcr}}\,\right.

(5.25)

In particular

q({t_{0}})=\Psi^{t_{1}}_{p_{t_{1}}}({t_{0}})={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}({t_{1}})=p({t_{1}})
and

q({t_{1}})=\Psi^{t_{1}}_{p_{t_{1}}}({t_{1}})={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}({t_{0}})=p({t_{0}})
.

Proposition 5.12

The velocity on the trajectory traveled in the opposite direction is
the opposite of the velocity on the initial trajectory:

{d\Psi^{t_{1}}_{p_{t_{1}}}\over du}(u)=q^{\prime}(u)=-p^{\prime}(t)=-{\vec{v}}(t,p(t))\quad\hbox{when}\quad t={t_{0}}{+}{t_{1}}{-}u,

(5.26)

Proof.
\Psi^{t_{1}}_{p_{t_{1}}}(u)={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}({t_{0}}{+}{t_{1}}{-}u)
gives

{d\Psi^{t_{1}}_{p_{t_{1}}}\over du}(u)=-{d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}\over dt}({t_{0}}{+}{t_{1}}{-}u)=-{\vec{v}}({t_{0}}{+}{t_{1}}{-}u,{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}({t_{0}}{+}{t_{1}}{-}u))=-{\vec{v}}(t,{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t))
when
t={t_{0}}{+}{t_{1}}{-}u
.

5.7
Variation of the flow as a function of the initial time

5.7.1
Ambiguous and non ambiguous notations

Let
\Phi:(t,u,p)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rightarrow\Phi(t,u,p)\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
be a
C^{1}
function.
The partial derivatives are

\partial_{1}\Phi(t,u,p):=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{\Phi(t{+}h,u,p)-\Phi(t,u,p)\over h},

(5.27)

\partial_{2}\Phi(t,u,p):=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{\Phi(t,u{+}h,p)-\Phi(t,u,p)\over h},

(5.28)

and
\partial_{3}\Phi(t,u,p)
, defined for all
{\vec{w}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
(a vector at
p
) by,

\partial_{3}\Phi(t,u,p).{\vec{w}}:=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{\Phi(t,u,p{+}h{\vec{w}})-\Phi(t,u,p)\over h}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}d\Phi(t,u,p).{\vec{w}},

(5.29)

When the name of the first variable is systematically noted
t
, then

\partial_{1}\Phi(t,u,p){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\partial\Phi\over\partial t}(t,u,p){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\partial\Phi(t,u,p)\over\partial t}.

(5.30)

NB: This notation can be ambiguous:
What is the meaning of
{\partial\Phi\over\partial t}(t;t,p)
?
In ambiguous situations, use the notation
\partial_{1}\Phi
, or (if no composed functions inside) use

{\partial\Phi(t,u,p)\over\partial t}_{|u=t}

(so
t
is the derivation variable, and after the calculation you take
u=t
).

When the name of the second variable is systematically noted
u
, then

\partial_{2}\Phi(t,u,p){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\partial\Phi\over\partial u}(t,u,p){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\partial\Phi(t,u,p)\over\partial u}.

(5.31)

NB: Idem this notation can be ambiguous:
What is the meaning of
{\partial\Phi\over\partial u}(u;u,p)
?
In ambiguous situations, use the notation
\partial_{2}\Phi
, or use

{\partial\Phi(t,u,p)\over\partial u}_{|t=u}
.

When the name of the third variable is systematically a space variable noted
p
, then

\partial_{3}\Phi(t,u,p){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}d\Phi(t,u,p){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\partial\Phi\over\partial p}(t,u,p){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\partial\Phi(t,u,p)\over\partial p}.

(5.32)

5.7.2
Variation of the flow as a function of the initial time

The law of composition of the flows gives (
5.19
) gives
\Phi(t;u,\Phi(u;{t_{0}},p_{0}))=\Phi(t;{t_{0}},p_{0})
.
Thus the derivative in
u
gives

\displaystyle{{}\partial_{2}\Phi(t;u,\Phi(u;{t_{0}},p_{0}))+d\Phi(t;u,\Phi(u;{t_{0}},p_{0})).\partial_{1}\Phi(u;{t_{0}},p_{0})=0,}

i.e.

\displaystyle{{}\partial_{2}\Phi(t;u,p(u))=-d\Phi(t;u,p(u)).{\vec{v}}(u,p(u))\quad\hbox{when}\quad p(u)=\Phi(u;{t_{0}},p_{0}).}

(5.33)

In particular
u={t_{0}}
gives, for all
(t,{t_{0}},p_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
,

({\partial\Phi(t;{t_{0}},p_{0})\over\partial{t_{0}}}=)\quad\partial_{2}\Phi(t;{t_{0}},p_{0})=-d\Phi(t;{t_{0}},p_{0}).{\vec{v}}({t_{0}},p_{0}).

(5.34)

In particular

({d\Phi(t;{t_{0}},p_{0})\over d{t_{0}}}_{|t={t_{0}}}=)\quad\partial_{2}\Phi({t_{0}};{t_{0}},p_{0})=-{\vec{v}}({t_{0}},p_{0}).

(5.35)

Part II
Push-forward

6
Push-forward

The general tool to describe “transport” is “push-forward by a motion” (the “take with you” operator), cf. §
4.1
and figure
4.1
.
The push-forward also gives the tool needed to understand the velocity addition formula:
In that case, the push-forward is the translator between observers.
The push-forward can also be used to write coordinate systems.
As usual, we start with qualitative results (observer independent results); Then, quantitative results are deduced.

6.1
Definition

{\cal E}
and
{\cal F}
are affine spaces,

E
and
F
are the associated vector spaces equipped with norms
||.||_{E}
and
||.||_{F}
with
\dim E=\dim F=n
,

{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}
and
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}}
are open sets in the affine space
{\cal E}
and
{\cal F}
,
or possibly the vector spaces
E
and
F
, and

\Psi:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}&\rightarrow{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}}\cr{p_{\!{\cal E}}}&\rightarrow{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})\quad\hbox{is a diffeomorphism}\crcr}}\,\right.

(6.1)

(a
C^{1}
invertible map which inverse is
C^{1}
),
called the push-forward, and
\Psi^{-1}
is the pull-back (push-forward with
\Psi^{-1}
).

Figure 6.1:
c_{\cal E}:s\rightarrow{p_{\!{\cal E}}}=c_{\cal E}(s)
is a curve in
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}
.
Push-forwarded by
\Psi
it becomes the curve
c_{{\cal E}*}:=\Psi\circ c_{\cal E}
in
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}}
.
The tangent vector at
{p_{\!{\cal E}}}=c_{\cal E}(s)
is
{\vec{w}}_{\cal E}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})=c_{\cal E}{}^{\prime}(s)
,
and the tangent vector at
{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=c_{\cal F}(s)=\Psi(c_{\cal E}(s))
is

{\vec{w}}_{{\cal E}*}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})=c_{\cal F}{}^{\prime}(s)=d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).{\vec{w}}_{\cal E}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
.
Other illustation: See figure
4.1
.

Example:
\Psi={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}:{\Omega_{t_{0}}}\rightarrow{\Omega_{t}}
, the motion that transforms
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
into
{\Omega_{t}}
,
cf. (
3.5
).

Example:
\Psi:U_{E}\rightarrow U_{F}
a coordinate system, see example
6.11
.

Example:
\Psi={\Theta_{t}}:{{\cal R}_{\!B}}\rightarrow{{\cal R}_{\!A}}
, a change of referential at
t
(change of observer), see §
10
.

6.2
Push-forward and pull-back of points

Definition 6.1

If
{p_{\!{\cal E}}}\in{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}
(a point in
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}
) then its push-forward by
\Psi
is the point

{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\framebox{$\displaystyle\Psi_{*}{p_{\!{\cal E}}}:=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})$}={p_{{\cal E}*}}\;\in{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}},

(6.2)

see figure
6.1
, the last notation if
\Psi
is implicit.
And if
{p_{\!{\cal F}}}\in{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}}
then its pull-back by
\Psi
is the point

{p_{\!{\cal E}}}=\framebox{$\displaystyle\Psi^{*}{p_{\!{\cal F}}}:=\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})$}={p_{\cal F}{}^{*}}\;\in{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}.

(6.3)

We immediately have
\Psi^{*}\circ\Psi_{*}=I
.

The notations
∗
for push-forward and
∗
for pull-back have been proposed by Spivak; Also see Abraham and Marsden
[
3
]
(second edition) who adopt this notation.

6.3
Push-forward and pull-back of curves

We push-forward (and pull-back) the points on a curve:

Definition 6.2

Let

c_{\cal E}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr]-\varepsilon,\varepsilon[&\rightarrow{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}\cr s&\rightarrow{p_{\!{\cal E}}}=c_{\cal E}(s)\crcr}}\,\right\}
be a curve in
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}
.
Its push-forward by
\Psi
is the curve

\Psi_{*}c_{\cal E}:=\Psi\circ c_{\cal E}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr]-\varepsilon,\varepsilon[&\rightarrow{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}}\cr s&\rightarrow{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi_{*}c_{\cal E}(s):=\Psi(c_{\cal E}(s)){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{c_{{\cal E}*}}(s)\quad(=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})),\crcr}}\,\right.

(6.4)

see figure
6.1
. (
\Psi_{*}c_{\cal E}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{c_{{\cal E}*}}
when
\Psi
is implicit.)
This defines

\Psi_{*}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\cal F}(]-\varepsilon,\varepsilon[;{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}})&\rightarrow{\cal F}(]-\varepsilon,\varepsilon[;{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}})\cr c_{\cal E}&\rightarrow\Psi_{*}(c_{\cal E}):=\Psi\circ c_{\cal E}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\Psi_{*}c_{\cal E}={c_{{\cal E}*}}.\crcr}}\,\right.

(6.5)

Definition 6.3

Let

c_{\cal F}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr]-\varepsilon,\varepsilon[&\rightarrow{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}}\cr s&\rightarrow{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=c_{\cal F}(s)\crcr}}\,\right\}

is a curve in
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}}
. Its pull-back by
\Psi
is

\Psi^{*}c_{\cal F}:=\Psi^{-1}\circ c_{\cal E}\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr]-\varepsilon,\varepsilon[&\rightarrow{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}\cr s&\rightarrow{p_{\!{\cal E}}}=\Psi^{*}c_{\cal F}(s):=\Psi^{-1}(c_{\cal F}(s)){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{c_{\cal F}{}^{*}}(s)\quad(=\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})).\crcr}}\,\right.

(6.6)

We have thus defined

\Psi^{*}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\cal F}(C^{1}(]-\varepsilon,\varepsilon[;{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}})&\rightarrow{\cal F}(C^{1}(]-\varepsilon,\varepsilon[;{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}})\cr c_{\cal F}&\rightarrow\Psi^{*}(c_{\cal F}):=\Psi^{-1}\circ c_{\cal F}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\Psi^{*}c_{\cal F}={c_{\cal F}{}^{*}}.\crcr}}\,\right.

(6.7)

6.4
Push-forward and pull-back of scalar functions

6.4.1
Definitions

Definition 6.4

Let

{f_{\cal E}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr{p_{\!{\cal E}}}&\rightarrow{f_{\cal E}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})\crcr}}\,\right\}

(scalar valued function).
Its push-forward by
\Psi
is the (scalar valued) function

\Psi_{*}{f_{\cal E}}:={f_{\cal E}}\circ\Psi^{-1}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}}&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr{p_{\!{\cal F}}}&\rightarrow\Psi_{*}{f_{\cal E}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}):={f_{\cal E}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{f_{{\cal E}*}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})\quad\hbox{when}\quad{p_{\!{\cal E}}}=\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}),\crcr}}\,\right.

(6.8)

(noted
{f_{{\cal E}*}}
when
\Psi
is implicit), i.e.
\Psi_{*}{f_{\cal E}}(\Psi_{*}{p_{\!{\cal E}}}):={f_{\cal E}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
, or

{f_{{\cal E}*}}({p_{{\cal E}*}}):={f_{\cal E}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
when
{p_{{\cal E}*}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
.
We have thus defined

\Psi_{*}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\cal F}({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}};\mathbb{R})&\rightarrow{\cal F}({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}};\mathbb{R})\cr{f_{\cal E}}&\rightarrow{f_{\cal F}}:=\Psi_{*}({f_{\cal E}})={f_{\cal E}}\circ\Psi^{-1}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\Psi_{*}{f_{\cal E}},\crcr}}\,\right.

(6.9)

the notation
\Psi_{*}({f_{\cal E}})=\Psi_{*}{f_{\cal E}}
since
\Psi_{*}
is linear:

((f_{\cal E}+\lambda g_{\cal E})\circ\Psi^{-1})({p_{\!{\cal F}}})=(f_{\cal E}+\lambda g_{\cal E})({p_{\!{\cal E}}})=f_{\cal E}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})+\lambda g_{\cal E}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})=(f_{\cal E}\circ\Psi^{-1})({p_{\!{\cal F}}})+\lambda(g_{\cal E}\circ\Psi^{-1})({p_{\!{\cal F}}})

gives
\Psi_{*}(f_{\cal E}+\lambda g_{\cal E})=\Psi_{*}({f_{\cal E}})+\lambda\Psi_{*}(g_{\cal E})
.

Definition 6.5

Let

{f_{\cal F}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}}&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr{p_{\!{\cal F}}}&\rightarrow{f_{\cal F}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})\crcr}}\,\right\}
.
Its pull-back by
\Psi
is the push-forward by
\Psi^{-1}
, i.e. is

\Psi^{*}{f_{\cal F}}:={f_{\cal F}}\circ\Psi:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr{p_{\!{\cal E}}}&\rightarrow\Psi^{*}{f_{\cal F}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}):={f_{\cal F}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{f_{\cal F}{}^{*}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})\quad\hbox{when}\quad{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}),\crcr}}\,\right.

(6.10)

i.e.
\Psi^{*}{f_{\cal F}}(\Psi^{*}{p_{\!{\cal F}}}):={f_{\cal F}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})
,
i.e.
{f_{\cal F}{}^{*}}({p_{\cal F}{}^{*}}):={f_{\cal F}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})
when
{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi^{*}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})
.
We have thus defined

\Psi^{*}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\cal F}({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}};\mathbb{R})&\rightarrow{\cal F}({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}};\mathbb{R})\cr{f_{\cal F}}&\rightarrow\Psi^{*}({f_{\cal F}})={f_{\cal F}{}^{*}}:={f_{\cal F}}\circ\Psi{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\Psi^{*}{f_{\cal F}}.\crcr}}\,\right.

(6.11)

We immediately have

\Psi^{*}\circ\Psi_{*}=I\quad\hbox{and}\quad\Psi_{*}\circ\Psi^{*}=I
(the first
I
is the identity in
{\cal F}({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}};\mathbb{R})
, the second
I
is the identity in
{\cal F}({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}};\mathbb{R})
).

NB: We used the same notations
\Psi_{*}
and
\Psi^{*}
than for the push-forward and pull-backs of points: The context removes ambiguities.

6.4.2
Interpretation: Why is it useful?

E.g.: Let
{\widetilde{\Phi}}:\mathbb{R}\times{\it O\!b\!j}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
be a motion of an object
{\it O\!b\!j}
.
An observer records the temperature
\theta
at all
t\in[{t_{0}},T]
and all
p={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{\it O\!b\!j})
:
He gets
\theta:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\hbox{\large$\cal C$}=\bigcup_{t}(\{t\}\times{\Omega_{t}})&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr(t,p)&\rightarrow\theta(t,p)\crcr}}\,\right\}
a Eulerian scalar valued function,
cf. (
2.2
).
Then he chooses an initial time
{t_{0}}
and considers the associated motion
{\Phi^{t_{0}}}
, cf. (
3.1
),
and considers

\theta_{t_{0}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\Omega_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr{p_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow\theta_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}}):=\theta({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})\crcr}}\,\right\}

(snapshot of the temperatures at
{t_{0}}
in
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
).
The push-forward of
\theta_{t_{0}}
by
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
is
({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})_{*}\theta_{t_{0}}:=\theta_{t_{0}}\circ({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{-1}
defines the “memory function”

({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})_{*}\theta_{t_{0}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\Omega_{t}}&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr{p_{t}}&\rightarrow({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})_{*}\theta_{t_{0}}({p_{t}}):=\theta_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}})\quad\hbox{when}\quad{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}),\crcr}}\,\right.

(6.12)

And he writes
({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})_{*}\theta_{t_{0}}({p_{t}}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\theta_{{t_{0}}*}}(t,{p_{t}})
, so the memory transported is at
t
at
{p_{t}}
(along a trajectory) by

{\theta_{{t_{0}}*}}(t,p(t))=\theta_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}}).

(6.13)

Question:
Why do we introduce
{\theta_{{t_{0}}*}}
since we have
\theta_{t_{0}}
?

Answer:

An observer does not have the gift of temporal and/or spatial ubiquity;
He has to do with values at the actual time
t
and position
{p_{t}}
where he is (Newton and Einstein’s point of view).
So, when he was at
{t_{0}}
at
{p_{t_{0}}}
the observer wrote the value
\theta_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}})
on a piece of paper (for memory), puts the piece of paper is his pocket, then once at
t
at
p(t)={\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})
, he takes the paper out of his pocket, and renames the value he reads as

{\theta_{{t_{0}}*}}(t,{p_{t}})
because he is now at
t
at
{p_{t}}
.
And, now at
t
at
{p_{t}}
, he can compare the past and present value.
In particular the rate

{\theta(t,p(t))-{\theta_{{t_{0}}*}}(t,p(t))\over t-{t_{0}}}={\hbox{\footnotesize actual}(t,p(t))-\hbox{\footnotesize memory}_{*}(t,p(t))\over t-{t_{0}}}

(6.14)

is physically meaningful for one observer at
t
at
{p_{t}}
(no ubiquity gift required).
For scalar value functions, we get the usual rate

{\theta(t,p(t))-\theta({t_{0}},p({t_{0}}))\over t-{t_{0}}}
, but it isn’t that simple for vector valued functions.

And the limit
t\rightarrow{t_{0}}
in (
6.14
) defines the Lie derivative for scalar valued functions.

6.5
Push-forward and pull-back of vector fields

This is one of the most important concept for mechanical engineers.

6.5.1
A definition by approximation

Elementary introduction.
Let
{p_{\!{\cal E}}}
and
{q_{\!{\cal E}}}
be points in
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}
, and let
{p_{\!{\cal F}}}={p_{{\cal E}*}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
and
{q_{\!{\cal F}}}={q_{{\cal E}*}}=\Psi({q_{\!{\cal E}}})
in
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}}

be the push-forwards by
\Psi
cf. (
6.1
).
The first order Taylor expansion gives

(\Psi({q_{\!{\cal E}}})-\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})=)\quad{q_{\!{\cal F}}}-{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).({q_{\!{\cal E}}}-{p_{\!{\cal E}}})+o(||{q_{\!{\cal E}}}-{p_{\!{\cal E}}}||_{E}),

(6.15)

thus,

{\overrightarrow{{p_{\!{\cal F}}}{q_{\!{\cal F}}}}\over||\overrightarrow{{p_{\!{\cal E}}}{q_{\!{\cal E}}}}||_{E}}=d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).{\overrightarrow{{p_{\!{\cal E}}}{q_{\!{\cal E}}}}\over||\overrightarrow{{p_{\!{\cal E}}}{q_{\!{\cal E}}}}||_{E}}+o(1).

(6.16)

And the definition of the push-forward is obtained by “neglecting” the
o(1)
(limit as
{q_{\!{\cal E}}}\rightarrow{p_{\!{\cal E}}}
):

Definition 6.6

If
{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})\in E
is a vector at
{p_{\!{\cal E}}}\in U
then its push-forward by
\Psi

is the vector
{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}*}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\Psi_{*}{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})\in F

defined at
{p_{\!{\cal F}}}={p_{{\cal E}*}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})\in{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}}
by

{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})=\framebox{$\displaystyle{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}*}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}):=d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})$}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\Psi_{*}{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).

(6.17)

6.5.2
The definition of the push-forward of a vector field

To fully grasp the definition, and to avoid making interpretation errors as in §
4.3
(the unfortunate notation
d\vec{x}=F.d\vec{X}
), we use the following definition of “a vector”: It is a “tangent vector to a curve” (needed for surfaces and manifolds). Details:

\bullet

Let
c_{\cal E}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr]-\varepsilon,\varepsilon[&\rightarrow{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}\cr s&\rightarrow{p_{\!{\cal E}}}=c_{\cal E}(s)\crcr}}\,\right\}
be a
C^{1}
curve in
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}
.
Its tangent vector at
{p_{\!{\cal E}}}=c_{\cal E}(s)
is

{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}):=c_{\cal E}{}^{\prime}(s)\quad(=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{c_{\cal E}(s+h)-c_{\cal E}(s)\over h}),

(6.18)

see figure
6.1
.
This defines the function

{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\rm Im}(c_{\cal E})&\rightarrow E\cr{p_{\!{\cal E}}}&\rightarrow{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})\crcr}}\,\right\}

called a vector field along
{\rm Im}(c_{\cal E}){\subset}{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}
.

\bullet

The push-forward of
c_{\cal E}
by
\Psi
being the image curve
{c_{{\cal E}*}}=\Psi\circ c_{\cal E}
(the curve transformed by
\Psi
) cf. (
6.4
), its tangent vector at
{p_{\!{\cal F}}}={c_{{\cal E}*}}(s)
is

{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}*}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}):={c_{{\cal E}*}}{}^{\prime}(s)\quad\hbox{thus}\quad=d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).c_{\cal E}{}^{\prime}(s)=d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).

(6.19)

Thus we have defined the vector field
{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}*}}
along
{\rm Im}({c_{{\cal E}*}})
called the push-forward of
{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}
by
\Psi
.

With all the integral curves of a vector field defined in
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}
, we get:

Definition 6.7

The push-forward by
\Psi
of a
C^{0}
vector field
{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}&\rightarrow E\cr{p_{\!{\cal E}}}&\rightarrow{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})\crcr}}\,\right\}
is the vector field

\Psi_{*}{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}={\vec{w}_{{\cal E}*}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}}&\rightarrow F\cr{p_{\!{\cal F}}}&\rightarrow\framebox{$\displaystyle\Psi_{*}{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}):=d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})$}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}*}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})\quad\hbox{when}\quad{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}),\crcr}}\,\right.

(6.20)

see figure
6.1
. (
\Psi_{*}{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}*}}
if
\Psi
is implicit).
In other words,

\Psi_{*}{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}:=(d\Psi.{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}})\circ\Psi^{-1}.

(6.21)

This defines the map

\Psi_{*}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr C^{\infty}({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}};E)&\rightarrow C^{\infty}({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}};F)\cr{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}&\rightarrow\Psi_{*}({\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}):=\Psi_{*}{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}={\vec{w}_{{\cal E}*}}\crcr}}\,\right\}
.
(We use the same notation
\Psi_{*}
as in definition
6.4
for scalar valued functions: The context removes ambiguity.)

Remark 6.8

Unlike scalar functions, cf. §
6.4.2
: At
{t_{0}}
at
{p_{t_{0}}}

you cannot just draw a vector
{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}})
on a piece of paper, put the paper in your pocket, then let yourself be carried by the flow
\Psi={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
(push-forward), then, once arrived at
t
at
{p_{t}}
, take the paper out of your pocket and read it to get the push-forward: The direction and length of the vector
{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}(t,{p_{t}})
are modified by the flow (a vector is not just a collection of scalar components).

Exercice 6.9

Prove:

{\vec{c_{\cal E}}^{\prime\prime}}(s)=d{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}),

(6.22)

and

d{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}*}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})=d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).d{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})+d^{2}\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}),

(6.23)

and

\displaystyle{{c_{{\cal E}*}}^{\prime\prime}(s)=}

\displaystyle{{}d{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}*}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}*}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})\quad(=d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).{\vec{c_{\cal E}}^{\prime\prime}}(s)+d^{2}\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).{\vec{c_{\cal E}}^{\prime}}(s).{\vec{c_{\cal E}}^{\prime}}(s)).}

(6.24)

Answer
.
{\vec{c_{\cal E}}^{\prime}}(s)={\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({c_{\cal E}}(s))
gives
{\vec{c_{\cal E}}^{\prime\prime}}(s)=d{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({c_{\cal E}}(s)).{\vec{c_{\cal E}}^{\prime}}(s)
,
hence (
6.22
).

{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}*}}(\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}))=d\Phi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
by definition of
{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}*}}
, hence (
6.23
).

{c_{\cal F}}(s)=\Psi({c_{\cal E}}(s))

gives
{\vec{c_{\cal F}}^{\prime}}(s)=d\Psi({c_{\cal E}}(s)).{\vec{c_{\cal E}}^{\prime}}(s)=d\Psi({c_{\cal E}}(s)).{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({c_{\cal E}}(s))={\vec{w}_{{\cal E}*}}({c_{\cal F}}(s))
.
Thus
{\vec{c_{\cal F}}^{\prime\prime}}(s)=(d^{2}\Psi({c_{\cal E}}(s)).{\vec{c_{\cal E}}^{\prime}}(s)).{\vec{c_{\cal E}}^{\prime}}(s)+d\Psi({c_{\cal E}}(s)).{\vec{c_{\cal E}}^{\prime\prime}}(s)=d{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}*}}({c_{\cal F}}(s)).{\vec{c_{\cal F}}^{\prime}}(s)
, hence (
6.24
).

6.5.3
Pull-back of a vector field

Definition 6.10

If
