{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}}&\rightarrow F\cr{p_{\!{\cal F}}}&\rightarrow{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})\crcr}}\,\right\}

is a vector field on
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}}
, then its pull-back by
\Psi
is the push-forward by
\Psi^{-1}
, i.e. is the vector field on
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}
defined by

\Psi^{*}{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}&\rightarrow E\cr{p_{\!{\cal E}}}&\rightarrow\framebox{$\displaystyle\Psi^{*}{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}):=d\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})$}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}{}^{*}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}),\quad\hbox{when}\quad{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).\crcr}}\,\right.

(6.25)

In other words,

\Psi^{*}{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}}:=(d\Psi^{-1}.{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}})\circ\Psi{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}{}^{*}}.

(6.26)

And we get

\Psi^{*}\circ\Psi_{*}=I\quad\hbox{and}\quad\Psi_{*}\circ\Psi^{*}=I.

(6.27)

Indeed,
\Psi^{*}(\Psi_{*}{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}})({p_{\!{\cal E}}})=d\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).\Psi_{*}{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})=d\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})={\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
, for all
{p_{\!{\cal E}}}
. Idem for the second equality.

6.6
Quantification with bases

6.6.1
Usual result

({\vec{a}}_{i})
is a Cartesian basis in
E
,

O_{\cal F}
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
are an origin in
{\cal F}
and a Cartesian basis in
F
,

{p_{\!{\cal E}}}\in{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}
,

{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})=O_{\cal F}+{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\psi_{i}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})\,{\vec{b}}_{i},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad[\overrightarrow{O_{\cal F}{p_{\!{\cal F}}}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}=\pmatrix{\psi_{1}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})\cr\vdots\cr\psi_{n}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})}.

(6.28)

Then, if
{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}
is a vector field in
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}
and
{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}=\sum_{i}w_{j}{\vec{a}}_{i}
, we get

\Psi_{*}{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})=d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}(d\psi_{i}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}))\,{\vec{b}}_{i}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}w_{j}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})(d\psi_{i}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).{\vec{a}}_{j})\,{\vec{b}}_{i}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}{\partial\psi_{i}\over\partial x_{j}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})w_{j}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})\,{\vec{b}}_{i}
, so

[\Psi_{*}{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})]_{|{\vec{b}}}=[d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}.[{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})]_{|{\vec{a}}},

(6.29)

where
[d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}=[d\psi_{i}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).{\vec{a}}_{j}]{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}[{\partial\psi_{i}\over\partial x_{j}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})]

is the Jacobian matrix.

6.6.2
Example: Polar coordinate system

Example 6.11

Change of coordinate system interpreted as a push-forward:
Paradigmatic example of the polar coordinate system (model generalized for the parametrization of any manifold).

Parametric Cartesian vector space
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{p}=\{{\vec{q}}=(r,\theta)\}
,
with its canonical basis
({\vec{a}}_{1},{\vec{a}}_{2})
, and
{\vec{q}}=r{\vec{a}}_{1}+\theta{\vec{a}}_{2}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}(r,\theta)
, so
[{\vec{q}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}=\pmatrix{r\cr\theta}
.
Geometric affine space
\mathbb{R}^{2}
(of positions),
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, associated vector space
{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{2}}}
,
O\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
(origin),

{\vec{x}}=\overrightarrow{Op}
, and a Euclidean basis
({\vec{b}}_{1},{\vec{b}}_{2})
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{2}}}
.
The “polar coordinate system” is the associated map

\Psi:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\vec{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{*}\times\mathbb{R}\subset\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{p}&\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{2}}}\cr{\vec{q}}=(r,\theta)&\rightarrow{\vec{x}}=\Psi({\vec{q}})=\Psi(r,\theta),\cr}}\,\right\}

defined by

{\vec{x}}=\Psi({\vec{q}}):=r\cos\theta\,{\vec{b}}_{1}+r\sin\theta\,{\vec{b}}_{2},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}=\pmatrix{x=r\cos\theta\cr y=r\sin\theta}.

(6.30)

The
i
-th coordinate line at
{\vec{q}}
in
\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{p}
(parametric space) is the straight line

{\vec{c}}_{{\vec{q}},i}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\mathbb{R}&\rightarrow\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{p}\cr s&\rightarrow{\vec{c}}_{{\vec{q}},i}(s)={\vec{q}}+s{\vec{a}}_{i}\crcr}}\,\right\}
,
and its tangent vector at
{\vec{c}}_{{\vec{q}},i}(s)
is
{\vec{c}}_{{\vec{q}},i}{}^{\prime}(s)={\vec{a}}_{i}
for all
s
.
This line is transformed by
\Psi
into the curve

\Psi_{*}(c_{q,i})=\Psi\circ{\vec{c}}_{{\vec{q}},i}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}c_{{\vec{x}},i}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\mathbb{R}&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}\cr s&\rightarrow c_{{\vec{x}},i}(s)=\Psi({\vec{q}}+s{\vec{a}}_{i})\crcr}}\,\right\}
(in particular
c_{{\vec{x}},i}(0)={\vec{x}}
). So

[\overrightarrow{Oc_{{\vec{x}},1}(s)}]_{|{\vec{b}}}=\pmatrix{(r{+}s)\cos\theta\cr(r{+}s)\sin\theta}\quad\hbox{(straight line)},\quad\hbox{and}\quad[\overrightarrow{Oc_{{\vec{x}},2}(s)}]_{|{\vec{b}}}=\pmatrix{r\cos(\theta{+}s)\cr r\sin(\theta{+}s)}\quad\hbox{(circle)}.

(6.31)

And the tangent vector at
c_{{\vec{x}},i}(s)
is
c_{{\vec{x}},i}{}^{\prime}(s){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{a}}_{i*}({\vec{x}})

(push-forward by
\Psi
), so

\displaystyle{{}{\vec{a}}_{1*}({\vec{x}}):=\Psi_{*}{\vec{a}}_{1}({\vec{x}})=d\Psi({\vec{q}}).{\vec{a}}_{1}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{\Psi({\vec{q}}{+}h{\vec{a}}_{1})-\Psi({\vec{q}})\over h}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{\Psi(r{+}h,\theta)-\Psi(r,\theta)\over h}={\partial\Psi\over\partial r}({\vec{q}}),}

\displaystyle{{}{\vec{a}}_{2*}({\vec{x}}):=\Psi_{*}{\vec{a}}_{2}({\vec{x}})=d\Psi({\vec{q}}).{\vec{a}}_{2}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{\Psi({\vec{q}}{+}h{\vec{a}}_{2})-\Psi({\vec{q}})\over h}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{\Psi(r,\theta{+}h)-\Psi(r,\theta)\over h}={\partial\Psi\over\partial\theta}({\vec{q}}),}

(6.32)

Thus

{\vec{a}}_{1*}({\vec{x}})=\cos\theta{\vec{b}}_{1}+\sin\theta{\vec{b}}_{2}\quad\hbox{and}\quad{\vec{a}}_{2*}({\vec{x}})=-r\sin\theta{\vec{b}}_{1}+r\cos\theta{\vec{b}}_{2},

(6.33)

i.e.

[{\vec{a}}_{1*}({\vec{x}})]_{|{\vec{b}}}=\pmatrix{\cos\theta\cr\sin\theta}\quad\hbox{and}\quad[{\vec{a}}_{2*}({\vec{x}})]_{|{\vec{b}}}=\pmatrix{-r\sin\theta\cr r\cos\theta}.

(6.34)

The basis
({\vec{a}}_{1*}({\vec{x}}),{\vec{a}}_{2*}({\vec{x}}))
is called the basis of the polar coordinate system at
{\vec{x}}

(it is orthogonal but not orthonormal since
||{\vec{a}}_{2*}({\vec{x}})||=r\neq 1
in general);
And
[d\Psi({\vec{q}})]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}=\pmatrix{[{\partial\Psi\over\partial r}({\vec{q}})]_{|{\vec{b}}}&[{\partial\Psi\over\partial\theta}({\vec{q}})]_{|{\vec{b}}}}=\pmatrix{[{\vec{a}}_{1*}({\vec{x}})]_{|{\vec{b}}}&[{\vec{a}}_{2*}({\vec{x}})]_{|{\vec{b}}}}=\pmatrix{\cos\theta&-r\sin\theta\cr\sin\theta&r\cos\theta}=[{\partial\Psi^{i}\over\partial q^{j}}({\vec{q}})]
is the Jacobian matrix of
\Psi
at
{\vec{q}}
.

And the dual basis of the polar system basis
({\vec{a}}_{1*}({\vec{x}}),{\vec{a}}_{2*}({\vec{x}}))
is called
(dq_{1}({\vec{x}}),dq_{2}({\vec{x}}))

(defined by
dq_{i}({\vec{x}}).{\vec{a}}_{j*}({\vec{x}})=\delta_{ij}
),
so

dq_{1}({\vec{x}})=\cos\theta\,dx_{1}+\sin\theta\,dx_{2}\quad\hbox{and}\quad dq_{2}({\vec{x}})=-{1\over r}\sin\theta\,dx_{1}+{1\over r}\cos\theta\,dx_{2},

(6.35)

i.e.
[dq_{1}({\vec{x}})]_{|{\vec{b}}}=\pmatrix{\cos\theta&\sin\theta}
and

[dq_{2}({\vec{x}})]_{|{\vec{b}}}=-{1\over r}\pmatrix{\sin\theta&\cos\theta}
(row matrices) when
{\vec{x}}=\Psi({\vec{q}})
.

Remark 6.12

The components
\gamma^{k}_{ij}({\vec{x}})
of the vector
d{\vec{a}}_{j*}({\vec{x}}).{\vec{a}}_{i*}({\vec{x}})\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{2}}}
in the basis
({\vec{a}}_{i*}({\vec{x}}))
are the Christoffel symbols of the polar coordinate system (with duality notations as it is usually presented):

d{\vec{a}}_{j*}({\vec{x}}).{\vec{a}}_{i*}({\vec{x}})={\sum_{k=1}^{n}}\gamma^{k}_{ij}({\vec{x}}){\vec{a}}_{k*}({\vec{x}}).

(6.36)

At
{\vec{x}}=\Psi({\vec{q}})
, with
{\vec{a}}_{j*}({\vec{x}})=d\Psi({\vec{q}}).{\vec{a}}_{j}
, i.e.
({\vec{a}}_{j*}\circ\Psi)({\vec{q}})={\partial\Psi\over\partial q^{j}}
,
we get

d{\vec{a}}_{j*}({\vec{x}}).{\vec{a}}_{i*}({\vec{x}})={\partial^{2}\Psi\over\partial q^{i}\partial q^{j}}({\vec{q}})=d{\vec{a}}_{i*}({\vec{x}}).{\vec{a}}_{j*}({\vec{x}}),\quad\hbox{so}\quad\gamma^{k}_{ij}=\gamma^{k}_{ji}

(6.37)

for all
i,j
(symmetry of the bottom indices as soon as
\Psi
is
C^{2}
).

Here for the polar coordinates,
{\partial\Psi\over\partial r}({\vec{q}})=\cos\theta{\vec{b}}_{1}+\sin\theta{\vec{b}}_{2}
gives

{\partial^{2}\Psi\over\partial r^{2}}({\vec{q}})=\vec{0}
, thus
\gamma^{1}_{11}=\gamma^{2}_{11}=0
, and

{\partial^{2}\Psi\over\partial\theta\partial r}({\vec{q}})=-\sin\theta{\vec{b}}_{1}+\cos\theta{\vec{b}}_{2}={1\over r}{\vec{a}}_{2*}({\vec{x}})
,
thus
\gamma^{1}_{12}=0=\gamma^{1}_{21}
and
\gamma^{2}_{12}={1\over r}=\gamma^{2}_{21}
.
And
{\partial\Psi\over\partial\theta}({\vec{q}})=-r\sin\theta{\vec{b}}_{1}+r\cos\theta{\vec{b}}_{2}
gives

{\partial^{2}\Psi\over\partial\theta^{2}}({\vec{q}})=-r\cos\theta{\vec{b}}_{1}-r\sin\theta{\vec{b}}_{2}=-r{\vec{a}}_{1*}({\vec{x}})
,
thus
\gamma^{1}_{22}=-r
and
\gamma^{2}_{22}=0
.

Remark 6.13

The (widely used) normalized polar coordinate basis
({\vec{n}}_{1}({\vec{x}}),{\vec{n}}_{2}({\vec{x}}))=({\vec{a}}_{1*}({\vec{x}}),{1\over r}{\vec{a}}_{2*}({\vec{x}}))
is not holonomic, i.e. is not the basis of a coordinate system (and its use makes higher derivation formulas complicated).
Indeed
{\vec{n}}_{2}({\vec{x}})={1\over r}{\vec{a}}_{2*}({\vec{x}})

gives

d{\vec{n}}_{2}({\vec{x}}).{\vec{n}}_{1}({\vec{x}})=(d({1\over r})({\vec{x}}).{\vec{n}}_{1}({\vec{x}})){\vec{a}}_{2*}({\vec{x}})+{1\over r}d{\vec{a}}_{2*}({\vec{x}}).{\vec{n}}_{1}({\vec{x}})
,
and
{\vec{n}}_{1}({\vec{x}})={\vec{a}}_{1*}({\vec{x}})
gives
d{\vec{n}}_{1}({\vec{x}}).{\vec{n}}_{2}({\vec{x}})=d{\vec{a}}_{1*}({\vec{x}}).({1\over r}{\vec{a}}_{2*})
,
thus

d{\vec{n}}_{2}({\vec{x}}).{\vec{n}}_{1}({\vec{x}})-d{\vec{n}}_{1}({\vec{x}}).{\vec{n}}_{2}({\vec{x}})=(d({1\over r})({\vec{x}}).{\vec{n}}_{1}({\vec{x}})){\vec{a}}_{2*}({\vec{x}})\neq\vec{0}
, since
{1\over r}=(x^{2}+y^{2})^{-{1\over 2}}
gives

d({1\over r})({\vec{x}}).{\vec{n}}_{1}({\vec{x}})=\pmatrix{-x(x^{2}+y^{2})^{-{3\over 2}}&-y(x^{2}+y^{2})^{-{3\over 2}}}.\pmatrix{\cos\theta\cr\sin\theta}={1\over r^{3}}(-r\cos^{2}\theta-r\sin^{2}\theta)={-1\over r^{2}}\neq 0
.

Remark 6.14

(Pay attention to the notations.)
Let
f:{\vec{q}}\in\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{p}\rightarrow f({\vec{q}})\in\mathbb{R}
be
C^{2}
.
Call
g
its push-forward by
\Psi
, i.e.
g:{\vec{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow g({\vec{x}})=f({\vec{q}})\in\mathbb{R}
when
{\vec{x}}=\Psi({\vec{q}})
.
So
f({\vec{q}})=(g\circ\Psi)({\vec{q}})
and

df({\vec{q}}).{\vec{a}}_{j}=dg(\Psi({\vec{q}})).d\Psi({\vec{q}}).{\vec{a}}_{j}=dg({\vec{x}}).{\vec{a}}_{j*}({\vec{x}}).

(6.38)

With
df({\vec{q}}).{\vec{a}}_{j}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\partial f\over\partial q^{j}}({\vec{q}})

and
dg({\vec{x}}).{\vec{b}}_{j}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\partial g\over\partial x^{j}}({\vec{x}})

and
{\vec{a}}_{j*}({\vec{x}})=d\Psi({\vec{q}}).{\vec{a}}_{j}=\sum_{i}{\partial\Psi^{i}\over\partial q^{j}}({\vec{q}}){\vec{a}}_{j}
, we get

{\partial f\over\partial q^{j}}({\vec{q}})=\sum_{i}{\partial g\over\partial x^{i}}({\vec{x}}){\partial\Psi^{i}\over\partial q^{j}}({\vec{q}}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\partial g\over\partial q^{j}}({\vec{x}})\;\;...\;(!!)

(6.39)

Mind this notation!!
g
is a function of
{\vec{x}}
, not of
{\vec{q}}
, so

\displaystyle{\partial g\over\partial q^{i}}({\vec{x}})\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize means}}{\partial f\over\partial q^{i}}({\vec{q}})
,
i.e.
\displaystyle{\partial g\over\partial q^{i}}({\vec{x}})\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize means}}{\partial(g\circ\Psi)\over\partial q^{i}}({\vec{q}})
…
which is
[df({\vec{q}})]=[dg({\vec{x}})].[d\Psi({\vec{q}})
…

Then (with
f
and
\Psi

C^{2}
)

\displaystyle{{\partial{\partial g\over\partial q^{i}}\over\partial q^{j}}({\vec{x}})\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize means}}{\partial{\partial(g\circ\Psi)\over\partial q^{i}}\over\partial q^{j}}({\vec{q}})=}

\displaystyle{{}d(dg.{\vec{a}}_{i*})({\vec{x}}).d\Psi({\vec{q}}).{\vec{a}}_{j}=d(dg.{\vec{a}}_{i*})({\vec{x}}).{\vec{a}}_{j^{*}}({\vec{x}})}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}d((dg({\vec{x}}).{\vec{a}}_{j^{*}}({\vec{x}})).{\vec{a}}_{i*}({\vec{x}})+dg({\vec{x}}).(d{\vec{a}}_{i*}({\vec{x}}).{\vec{a}}_{j}({\vec{x}})){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\partial^{2}g\over\partial q^{i}\partial q^{j}}({\vec{x}}).}

(6.40)

So

{\partial^{2}g\over\partial q^{i}\partial q^{j}}({\vec{x}})\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize means}}d^{2}g({\vec{x}})({\vec{a}}_{i*}({\vec{x}}),{\vec{a}}_{j*}({\vec{x}}))+{\sum_{k=1}^{n}}{\partial g\over\partial x^{k}}({\vec{x}})\gamma^{k}_{ij}({\vec{x}}){\vec{a}}_{k}({\vec{x}}),

(6.41)

and
{\partial^{2}g\over\partial q^{i}\partial q^{j}}({\vec{x}})
is
not
reduced to
d^{2}g({\vec{x}})({\vec{a}}_{i*}({\vec{x}}),{\vec{a}}_{j*}({\vec{x}}))
(the Christoffel symbols have appeared): First order derivatives
{\partial g\over\partial x^{k}}
are still alive.
(Contrary to
{\partial^{2}g\over\partial x^{i}\partial x^{j}}({\vec{x}})=d^{2}g({\vec{x}})({\vec{b}}_{i},{\vec{b}}_{j})
with a Cartesian basis
({\vec{b}}_{i})
.)

NB: The independent variables
r
and
\theta
don’t have the same dimension (a length and an angle):
There is no physical meaningful inner dot product in the parameter space
\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{p}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}=\{(r,\theta)\}
,
but this space is very useful… (As in thermodynamics: No meaningful inner dot product in the
(T,P)
space.)

7
Push-forward and pull-back of differential forms

7.1
Definition

Setting of §
6.1
.
Consider a differential form
{\alpha_{\cal E}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}&\rightarrow{E^{*}}={\cal L}(E;\mathbb{R})\cr{p_{\!{\cal E}}}&\rightarrow{\alpha_{\cal E}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})\crcr}}\,\right\}
on
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}
(a field of linear forms), and a vector field

{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}&\rightarrow E\cr{p_{\!{\cal E}}}&\rightarrow{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})\crcr}}\,\right\}
.
Hence

{f_{\cal E}}={\alpha_{\cal E}}.{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr{p_{\!{\cal E}}}&\rightarrow{f_{\cal E}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})=(\alpha.{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}})({p_{\!{\cal E}}})={\alpha_{\cal E}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})\crcr}}\,\right.

is a scalar valued function (value of
{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}
given by
{\alpha_{\cal E}}
).
And (
6.8
) gives (push-forward
{f_{\cal E}}={\alpha_{\cal E}}.{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}
by
\Psi
)

\Psi_{*}({\alpha_{\cal E}}.{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}})({p_{\!{\cal F}}})=({\alpha_{\cal E}}.{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}})({p_{\!{\cal E}}})={\alpha_{\cal E}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})\quad\hbox{when}\quad{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).

(7.1)

With
{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}*}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})=d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
cf. (
6.20
) (push-forward of
{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}
), we get

\Psi_{*}({\alpha_{\cal E}}.{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}})({p_{\!{\cal F}}})=\underbrace{{\alpha_{\cal E}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})^{-1}}_{{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\alpha_{{\cal E}*}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})}.{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})\quad\hbox{when}\quad{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})\;:

(7.2)

Definition 7.1

The push-forward of a differential form
{\alpha_{\cal E}}\in\Omega^{1}({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}})
is the differential form
\in\Omega^{1}({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}})
given by

\Psi_{*}{\alpha_{\cal E}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}}&\rightarrow F^{*}={\cal L}(F;\mathbb{R})\cr{p_{\!{\cal F}}}&\rightarrow\framebox{$\displaystyle\Psi_{*}{\alpha_{\cal E}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}):={\alpha_{\cal E}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})^{-1}$}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\alpha_{{\cal E}*}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})\quad\hbox{when}\quad{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}),\crcr}}\,\right.

(7.3)

the last notation when
\Psi
is implicit.
In other words,
\Psi_{*}{\alpha_{\cal E}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})={\alpha_{\cal E}}(\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})).d\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})
, i.e.

\Psi_{*}{\alpha_{\cal E}}:=({\alpha_{\cal E}}\circ\Psi^{-1}).d\Psi^{-1}.

(7.4)

(Once again, we used the same notation
\Psi_{*}
than for the push-forward of vector fields and functions: The context removes any ambiguities.)

Remark 7.2

We cannot always see a vector field (e.g. we can’t see an internal force field): To know it we need to measure it with a well defined tool, the tool being here a differential form; And the definition
7.1
is a compatbility definition so that we can recover the push-forward of the vector field.

Definition 7.3

The pull-forward of a a differential form
{\alpha_{\!{\cal F}}}\in\Omega^{1}({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}})
is the differential form

\Psi^{*}{\alpha_{\!{\cal F}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}&\rightarrow{\cal L}(E;\mathbb{R})\cr{p_{\!{\cal E}}}&\rightarrow\Psi^{*}{\alpha_{\!{\cal F}}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}):={\alpha_{\!{\cal F}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\alpha_{\!{\cal F}}}{}^{*}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})\quad\hbox{when}\quad{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}),\crcr}}\,\right.

(7.5)

In other words,

\Psi^{*}{\alpha_{\!{\cal F}}}:=({\alpha_{\!{\cal F}}}\circ\Psi).d\Psi.

(7.6)

(For an alternative definition, see remark
7.5
.)

Proposition 7.4

For all
{\alpha_{\cal E}}\in\Omega^{1}({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}})
and
{\alpha_{\!{\cal F}}}\in\Omega^{1}({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}})
(differential forms),
and
{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}\in\Gamma({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}})
and
{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}}\in\Gamma({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}})
(vector fields), we have (objectivity result)

(\Psi_{*}{\alpha_{\cal E}})({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})={\alpha_{\cal E}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).(\Psi_{*}{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}})({p_{\!{\cal E}}})\quad\hbox{when}\quad{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}),

(7.7)

i.e.
{\alpha_{{\cal E}*}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})={\alpha_{\cal E}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}{}^{*}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
. In particular with
{\alpha_{\cal E}}=df
(exact differential form) where
f\in C^{1}({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}};\mathbb{R})
,

d(\Psi_{*}f)=\Psi_{*}(df).

(7.8)

(This commutativity result is very particular to the case
\alpha=df
: In general

d(\Psi_{*}T)\neq\Psi_{*}(dT)
for a tensor of order
\geq 2
, see e.g. (
8.19
)).

Proof.
{\alpha_{{\cal E}*}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})=({\alpha_{\cal E}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).d\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})).{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})={\alpha_{\cal E}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).(d\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}))={\alpha_{\cal E}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}}^{*}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
, for all
{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})\in{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}}
.

And

\Psi_{*}f({p_{\!{\cal F}}}):=f({p_{\!{\cal E}}})=f(\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}))
,
thus
d(\Psi_{*}f)({p_{\!{\cal F}}})=df({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).d\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})=\Psi_{*}(df)({p_{\!{\cal F}}})
.

And we have

\Psi^{*}\circ\Psi_{*}=I\quad\hbox{and}\quad\Psi_{*}\circ\Psi^{*}=I.

(7.9)

Indeed

\Psi^{*}(\Psi_{*}{\alpha_{\cal E}})({p_{\!{\cal E}}})=\Psi_{*}{\alpha_{\cal E}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})={\alpha_{\cal E}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).d\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})={\alpha_{\cal E}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
.
Idem for
\Psi_{*}\circ\Psi^{*}=I
.

Remark 7.5

The pull-back
{\alpha_{\!{\cal F}}}^{*}
can also be defined thanks to
the natural canonical isomorphism

\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\cal L}(E;F)&\rightarrow{\cal L}({F^{*}};{E^{*}})\cr L&\rightarrow L^{*}\crcr}}\,\right\}

given by
L^{*}(\ell_{F}).{\vec{u}}_{E}=\ell_{F}.(L.{\vec{u}}_{E})
for all
({\vec{u}}_{E},\ell_{F})\in E\times F^{*}
,
and
L^{*}(\ell_{F})=\ell_{F}.L
is called the pull-back of
\ell_{F}
by
L
.
In particular with
\ell_{F}={\alpha_{\!{\cal F}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})
and
L=d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
we get

d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})^{*}({\alpha_{\!{\cal F}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}))={\alpha_{\!{\cal F}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
,
i.e. (
7.5
).

7.2
Incompatibility: Riesz representation and push-forward

A push-forward is independent of any inner dot product: It is objective.

But here we introduce inner dot products
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
in
E
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
in
F
, e.g. Euclidean dot products in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
and
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
(observer dependent therefore subjective), because some mechanical engineers can’t begin with their beloved Euclidean dot products.

Let
{\alpha_{\cal E}}\in\Omega^{1}({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}})
and call
\beta_{\cal F}:=\Psi_{*}{\alpha_{\cal E}}
its push-forward by
\Psi
, i.e.

\beta_{\cal F}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}):={\alpha_{\cal E}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})^{-1}\quad\hbox{when}\quad{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).

(7.10)

Then call
\vec{a}_{g}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})\in E
and
\vec{b}_{h}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})\in F
the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
-Riesz representation vectors of
{\alpha_{\cal E}}
and
\beta_{\cal F}
, so, for all
\vec{u}_{\cal E}\in\Gamma({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}})
and all
{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}}\in\Gamma({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}})
, in short,

{\alpha_{\cal E}}.\vec{u}_{\cal E}=(\vec{a}_{g},\vec{u}_{\cal E})_{g},\quad\hbox{and}\quad\beta_{\cal F}.{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}}=(\vec{b}_{h},{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}})_{h},

(7.11)

which means

{\alpha_{\cal E}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).\vec{u}_{\cal E}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})=(\vec{a}_{g}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}),\vec{u}_{\cal E}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}))_{g}
and
\beta_{\cal F}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})=(\vec{b}_{h}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}),{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}))_{h}
, for all
{p_{\!{\cal E}}}\in{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}
and
{p_{\!{\cal F}}}\in{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}}
. This defines the vector fields
\vec{a}_{g}\in\Gamma({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}})
and
\vec{b}_{h}\in\Gamma({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}})
.

Proposition 7.6

\vec{b}_{h}\neq\Psi_{*}\vec{a}_{g}
in general (although
\beta_{\cal F}=\Psi_{*}{\alpha_{\cal E}}
), because

\displaystyle{\vec{b}_{h}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})=}

\displaystyle{{}d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})^{-T}.\vec{a}_{g}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})}

\displaystyle{\neq}

\displaystyle{{}d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).\vec{a}_{g}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})\;\hbox{ in general}}

(7.12)

(unless
d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})^{-T}=d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
, i.e.
d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})^{T}.d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})^{-1}=I
, as a rigid body motion).

So the Riesz representation vector of the push-forwarded linear form is not the push-forwarded representation vector of the linear form push-forwarded.

This is not a surprise: A push-forward is independent of any inner dot product, while a Riesz representation vector depends on a chosen inner dot product (E.g. Euclidean foot? metre?).

So, as long as possible (not before you need to quantify), you should avoid using a Riesz representation vector, i.e. you should use the original (the qualitative differential form) as long as possible, and delay the use of a representative (quantification with which dot product?) as late as possible.

Proof.
Recall:
The transposed relative to
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
of the linear map
d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})\in{\cal L}(E;F)
is the linear map

d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})_{gh}^{T}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})^{T}\in{\cal L}(F;E)
defined by, for all
\vec{u}_{\cal E}\in E
and
{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}}\in F
vectors at
{p_{\!{\cal E}}}
and
{p_{\!{\cal F}}}
, cf. (
A.68
),

(d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})^{T}.{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}},\vec{u}_{\cal E})_{g}=({\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}},d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).\vec{u}_{\cal E})_{h}.

(7.13)

(
7.11
) gives, with
{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
,

\displaystyle{(\vec{a}_{g}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}),\vec{u}_{\cal E})_{g}=}

\displaystyle{{}{\alpha_{\cal E}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).\vec{u}_{\cal E}=\bigl(\beta_{\cal F}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})\bigr).\vec{u}_{\cal E}=\beta_{\cal F}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).\bigl(d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).\vec{u}_{\cal E}\bigr)}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}(\vec{b}_{h}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}),d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).\vec{u}_{\cal E})_{h}=(d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})^{T}.\vec{b}_{h}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}),\vec{u}_{\cal E})_{g},}

(7.14)

true for all
\vec{u}_{\cal E}
, thus
\vec{a}_{g}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})=d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})^{T}.\vec{b}_{h}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})
, thus (
7.12
).

8
Push-forward and pull-back of tensors

To lighten the presentation, we only deal with order 1 and 2 tensors.
Similar approach for any tensor.

8.1
Push-forward and pull-back of order 1 tensors

Proposition 8.1

If
T
is either a vector field or a differential form, then its push-forward satisfies,
for all
\xi
vector field or differential form (when required) in
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}}
,

\hbox{in short:}\quad(\Psi_{*}T)(\xi)=T(\Psi^{*}\xi),\quad\hbox{written}\quad\Psi_{*}T(.)=T(\Psi^{*}.),

(8.1)

i.e.
(\Psi_{*}T)({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).\xi({p_{\!{\cal F}}})=T({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).\Psi^{*}\xi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
when
{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
. Similarly:

\hbox{in short:}\quad(\Psi^{*}T)(\xi)=T(\Psi_{*}\xi),\quad\hbox{written}\quad\Psi^{*}T(.)=T(\Psi_{*}.),

(8.2)

i.e.
(\Psi^{*}T)({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).\xi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})=T({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).\Psi_{*}\xi({p_{\!{\cal F}}})
when
{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
.

Proof.
\bullet
Case
T={\alpha_{\cal E}}\in\Omega^{1}({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}})
(differential form = a
{0\choose 1}
tensor),
then here
\xi={\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}}\in\Gamma({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}})
and we have to check:
(\Psi_{*}{\alpha_{\cal E}})({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})={\alpha_{\cal E}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).\Psi^{*}{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
, i.e.
({\alpha_{\cal E}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).d\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})).{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})={\alpha_{\cal E}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).(d\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).{\vec{w}_{{\cal F}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}))
: True.

\bullet
Case
T={\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}\in\Gamma({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}})
(vector field
\simeq
a
{1\choose 0}
tensor), then here
\xi={\alpha_{\!{\cal F}}}\in\Omega^{1}({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}})
we have to check:

(\Psi_{*}{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}})({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).{\alpha_{\!{\cal F}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})={\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).\Psi^{*}({\alpha_{\!{\cal F}}})({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
,
where we implicitly use to the natural canonical isomorphism

{\cal J}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr E&\rightarrow{E^{**}}\cr{\vec{w}}&\rightarrow w{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{w}}\crcr}}\,\right\}

defined by
w(\ell)=\ell.{\vec{w}}
for all
\ell\in{E^{*}}
.
So we have to check:

{\alpha_{\!{\cal F}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).(\Psi_{*}{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}})({p_{\!{\cal F}}})=\Psi^{*}({\alpha_{\!{\cal F}}})({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
,
i.e.
{\alpha_{\!{\cal F}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).(d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}))=({\alpha_{\!{\cal F}}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})^{-1}).{\vec{w}_{{\cal E}}})({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
: True.

For (
8.2
), use
\Psi^{-1}
instead of
\Psi
.

8.2
Push-forward and pull-back of order 2 tensors

Definition 8.2

Let
T
be an order 2 tensor in
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}
. Its push-forward by
\Psi
is the order 2 tensor
\Psi_{*}T
in
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}}
defined by, for all
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
vector field or differential form (when required) in
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}}
,

\hbox{in short:}\quad\Psi_{*}T(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}):=T(\Psi^{*}\xi_{1},\Psi^{*}\xi_{2})\quad\hbox{written}\quad\Psi_{*}T(\cdot,\cdot):=T(\Psi^{*}\cdot,\Psi^{*}\cdot),

(8.3)

i.e.
\Psi_{*}T({p_{\!{\cal F}}})(\xi_{1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}),\xi_{2}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})):=T({p_{\!{\cal E}}})(\Psi^{*}\xi_{1}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}),\Psi^{*}\xi_{2}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}))
when
{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
.

Let
T
be an order 2 tensor in
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}}
. Its pull-back by
\Psi
is the order 2 tensor
\Psi^{*}T
in
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}
defined by, for all
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
vector field or differential form (when required) in
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}
,

\hbox{in short:}\quad\Psi^{*}T(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}):=T(\Psi_{*}\xi_{1},\Psi_{*}\xi_{2})\quad\hbox{written}\quad\Psi^{*}T(\cdot,\cdot):=T(\Psi_{*}\cdot,\Psi_{*}\cdot),

(8.4)

i.e.,

\Psi^{*}T({p_{\!{\cal E}}})(\xi_{1}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}),\xi_{2}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})):=T({p_{\!{\cal F}}})(\Psi_{*}\xi_{1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}),\Psi_{*}\xi_{2}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}))
when
{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
.

Example 8.3

If
T\in T^{0}_{2}({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}})
(e.g., a metric) then, for all vector fields
{{\vec{w}}_{1}},{{\vec{w}}_{2}}
in
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}}
,

\displaystyle{T_{*}({{\vec{w}}_{1}},{{\vec{w}}_{2}})\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqpftensg1})}}

\displaystyle{{}T({{\vec{w}}_{1}}{}^{*},{{\vec{w}}_{2}}{}^{*})=T(d\Psi^{-1}.{{\vec{w}}_{1}},d\Psi^{-1}.{{\vec{w}}_{2}}),}

(8.5)

i.e.,

T_{*}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})({{\vec{w}}_{1}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}),{{\vec{w}}_{2}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}))=T({p_{\!{\cal E}}})(d\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).{{\vec{w}}_{1}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}),d\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).{{\vec{w}}_{2}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}))
when
{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
.

Expression with bases
({\vec{a}}_{i})
in
E
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
in
F
: In short we have

(T_{*})_{ij}=T_{*}({\vec{b}}_{i},{\vec{b}}_{j})=T({\vec{b}}_{i}{}^{*},{\vec{b}}_{j}{}^{*})=[{\vec{b}}_{i}^{*}]_{|{\vec{a}}}^{T}.[T]_{|{\vec{a}}}.[{\vec{b}}_{j}^{*}]_{|{\vec{a}}}=([{\vec{b}}_{i}]_{|{\vec{b}}}^{T}.[d\Psi]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}^{-T}).[T]_{|{\vec{a}}}.([d\Psi]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}^{-1}.[{\vec{b}}_{j}]_{|{\vec{b}}})=([d\Psi]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}^{-T}.[T]_{|{\vec{a}}}.[d\Psi]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}^{-1})_{ij}
,
thus

[T_{*}]_{|{\vec{b}}}=[d\Psi]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}^{-T}.[T]_{|{\vec{a}}}.[d\Psi]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}^{-1},

(8.6)

which means

[(\Psi_{*}T)({p_{\!{\cal F}}})]_{|{\vec{b}}}=([d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}})^{-T}.[T({p_{\!{\cal E}}})]_{|{\vec{a}}}.([d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}})^{-1}
when
{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
.

Particular case of an elementary tensor
T=\alpha_{1}\otimes\alpha_{2}\in T^{0}_{2}({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}})
,
where
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\Omega^{1}({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}})
, so

T({\vec{u}}_{1},{\vec{u}}_{2})=(\alpha_{1}\otimes\alpha_{2})({\vec{u}}_{1},{\vec{u}}_{2})=(\alpha_{1}.{\vec{u}}_{1})(\alpha_{2}.{\vec{u}}_{2})
:
For all
{{\vec{w}}_{1}},{{\vec{w}}_{2}}\in\Gamma({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}})
,

\displaystyle{(\alpha_{1}\otimes\alpha_{2})_{*}({{\vec{w}}_{1}},{{\vec{w}}_{2}})\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqpftensg1})}(\alpha_{1}\otimes\alpha_{2})({{\vec{w}}_{1}}^{*},{{\vec{w}}_{2}}^{*})=(\alpha_{1}.{{\vec{w}}_{1}}^{*})(\alpha_{2}.{{\vec{w}}_{2}}^{*})\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqasw})}(\alpha_{1*}.{{\vec{w}}_{1}})(\alpha_{2*}.{{\vec{w}}_{2}}),}

(8.7)

thus

(\alpha_{1}\otimes\alpha_{2})_{*}=\alpha_{1*}\otimes\alpha_{2*}.

(8.8)

(And any tensor is a finite sum of elementary tensors.)

And for the pull-back: For all vector fields
{\vec{u}}_{1},{\vec{u}}_{2}
in
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}
,

\displaystyle{T^{*}({\vec{u}}_{1},{\vec{u}}_{2})\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqpftensg1})}}

\displaystyle{{}T({\vec{u}}_{1*},{\vec{u}}_{2*})=T(d\Psi.{\vec{u}}_{1},d\Psi.{\vec{u}}_{2}).}

(8.9)

Example 8.4

If
T\in T^{1}_{1}({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}})
then for all vector fields
{\vec{w}}\in\Gamma({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}})
and differential forms
\beta\in\Omega^{1}({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}})
,

\displaystyle{T_{*}(\beta,{\vec{w}})=T(\beta^{*},{\vec{w}}^{*})=T(\beta.d\Psi,d\Psi^{-1}.{\vec{w}}),}

(8.10)

i.e.,

T_{*}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})(\beta({p_{\!{\cal F}}}),{\vec{w}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}))=T({p_{\!{\cal E}}})(\beta({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}),d\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).{\vec{w}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}))
when
{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
.

For the elementary tensor
T={\vec{u}}\otimes\alpha\in T^{1}_{1}({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}})
,
made of the vector field
{\vec{u}}\in\Gamma({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}})
and of the differential form
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}})
:
For all
\beta,{\vec{w}}\in\Omega^{1}({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}})\times\Gamma({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}})
, in short,

\displaystyle{({\vec{u}}\otimes\alpha)_{*}(\beta,{\vec{w}})\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqpftensg1})}({\vec{u}}\otimes\alpha)(\beta^{*},{\vec{w}}^{*})=({\vec{u}}.\beta^{*})(\alpha.{\vec{w}}^{*})\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqasw})}({\vec{u}}_{*}.\beta)(\alpha_{*}.{\vec{w}})=({\vec{u}}_{*}\otimes\alpha_{*})(\beta,{\vec{w}}),}

(8.11)

thus

({\vec{u}}\otimes\alpha)_{*}={\vec{u}}_{*}\otimes\alpha_{*}.

(8.12)

Expression with bases
({\vec{a}}_{i})
in
E
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
in
F
: In short we have

(T_{*})_{ij}=T_{*}(b^{i},{\vec{b}}_{j})=T(\Psi^{*}(b^{i}),\Psi^{*}({\vec{b}}_{j}))=[\Psi^{*}(b^{i})].[T].[\Psi^{*}({\vec{b}}_{j})]=[b^{i}].[d\Psi].[T].[d\Psi^{-1}].[{\vec{b}}_{j}]=([d\Psi].[T].[d\Psi^{-1}])_{ij}
,
thus

[T_{*}]_{|{\vec{b}}}=[d\Psi]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}.[T]_{|{\vec{a}}}.[d\Psi]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}^{-1},

(8.13)

which means

[(\Psi_{*}T)({p_{\!{\cal F}}})]_{|{\vec{b}}}=[d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}.[T({p_{\!{\cal E}}})]_{|{\vec{a}}}.[d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}^{-1}
when
{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
.

8.3
Push-forward and pull-back of endomorphisms

We have the natural canonical isomorphism

{\cal J}_{2}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\cal L}(E;E)&\rightarrow{\cal L}(E^{*},E;\mathbb{R})\cr L&\rightarrow T_{L}={\cal J}_{2}(L)\quad\hbox{where}\quad T_{L}(\alpha,{\vec{u}}):=\alpha.L.{\vec{u}},\quad\forall(\alpha,{\vec{u}})\in{E^{*}}\times E.\crcr}}\,\right.

(8.14)

Thus
\Psi_{*}T_{L}(m,{\vec{w}})=T_{L}(\Psi^{*}m,\Psi^{*}{\vec{w}})=(\Psi^{*}m).L.(\Psi^{*}{\vec{w}})=m.d\Psi.L.d\Psi^{-1}.{\vec{w}}
, thus:

Definition 8.5

The push-forward by
\Psi
of a field of endomorphisms
L
on
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}

is the field of endomorphisms
\Psi_{*}L=L_{*}
on
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}}
defined by

\hbox{in short:}\quad\Psi_{*}L=\framebox{$\displaystyle L_{*}=d\Psi.L.d\Psi^{-1}$},

(8.15)

i.e.,
L_{*}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})=d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).L({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).d\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})
when
{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
.

Thus with bases we get
[L_{*}]_{|{\vec{b}}}=[d\Psi]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}.[L]_{|{\vec{a}}}.[d\Psi]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}^{-1}
,
“as in (
8.13
)”.

Example 8.6

Elementary field of endomorphisms
L=({\cal J}_{2})^{-1}({\vec{u}}\otimes\alpha)
, where
{\vec{u}}\in\Gamma(E)
and
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(E)
: So
T_{L}={\vec{u}}\otimes\alpha
and
L.{\vec{u}}_{2}=(\alpha.{\vec{u}}_{2}){\vec{u}}
for all
{\vec{u}}_{2}\in\Gamma({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}})
).
Thus
L_{*}.{\vec{w}}_{2}=d\Psi.L.d\Psi^{-1}.{\vec{w}}_{2}=d\Psi.L.{\vec{w}}_{2}{}^{*}=(\alpha.{\vec{w}}_{2}{}^{*})d\Psi.{\vec{u}}=(\alpha_{*}.{\vec{w}}_{2}){\vec{u}}_{*}
for all
{\vec{w}}_{2}\in\Gamma(E)
, thus
(T_{L})_{*}={\vec{u}}_{*}\otimes\alpha_{*}
.

Definition 8.7

Let
L
be a field of endomorphisms on
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}}
.
Its pull-back by
\Psi

is the field of endomorphisms
\Psi^{*}L=L^{*}
on
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}
defined by

\hbox{in short:}\quad\Psi^{*}L=\framebox{$\displaystyle L^{*}=d\Psi^{-1}.L.d\Psi$},

(8.16)

i.e.,

L^{*}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})=d\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).L({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
when
{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
.

8.4
Application to derivatives of vector fields

{\vec{u}}\in\Gamma({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}})
is a
C^{1}
vector field in
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}
),
{p_{\!{\cal E}}}\in{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}
,
so
d{\vec{u}}:{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}\rightarrow{\cal L}(E;E)
(given by

d{\vec{u}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).{\vec{w}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{{\vec{u}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}+h{\vec{w}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}))-{\vec{u}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})\over h}

for all
{\vec{w}}\in\Gamma({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}})
).
Thus its push-forward:

((d{\vec{u}})_{*}=)\quad\Psi_{*}(d{\vec{u}})=d\Psi.d{\vec{u}}.d\Psi^{-1}

(8.17)

i.e.
(d{\vec{u}})_{*}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})=d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).d{\vec{u}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})^{-1}
when
{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
.

8.5
\Psi_{*}(d{\vec{u}})
versus
d(\Psi_{*}{\vec{u}})
: No commutativity

Here
\Psi
is
C^{2}
,
{\vec{u}}\in\Gamma({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}})
,
{p_{\!{\cal E}}}\in{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}
,
{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
, so

\Psi_{*}{\vec{u}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})=d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).{\vec{u}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})=(d\Psi(\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})).({\vec{u}}(\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}))
, and, for all
{\vec{w}}\in\Gamma({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}})
,

d(\Psi_{*}{\vec{u}})({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).{\vec{w}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})=(d^{2}\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).(d\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).{\vec{w}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}))).{\vec{u}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})+d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).d{\vec{u}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).d\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).{\vec{w}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}),

(8.18)

with
\Psi_{*}(d{\vec{u}})({p_{\!{\cal F}}})=d\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).d{\vec{u}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).d\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})
, thus, in short,

d(\Psi_{*}{\vec{u}}).{\vec{w}}=\Psi_{*}(d{\vec{u}}).{\vec{w}}+d^{2}\Psi(\Psi^{*}{\vec{w}},{\vec{u}})\neq\Psi_{*}(d{\vec{u}})\quad\hbox{in general}.

(8.19)

So the differentiation
d
and the push-forward
∗
do not commute (
d(\Psi_{*}{\vec{u}})=\Psi_{*}(d{\vec{u}})
iff
\Psi
is affine).

8.6
Application to derivative of differential forms

Let
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}})
(a differential form on
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}
).
Its derivative
d\alpha:{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}\rightarrow{\cal L}(E;{E^{*}})
is given by

d\alpha({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).{\vec{u}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{\alpha({p_{\!{\cal E}}}+h{\vec{u}}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}))-\alpha({p_{\!{\cal E}}})\over h}\;\in{E^{*}}
,
for all
{\vec{u}}\in\Gamma({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}})
, i.e., for all
{\vec{u}}_{1},{\vec{u}}_{2}\in\Gamma({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}})
,

(d\alpha({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).{\vec{u}}_{1}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})).{\vec{u}}_{2}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{\alpha({p_{\!{\cal E}}}+h{\vec{u}}_{1}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})).{\vec{u}}_{2}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})-(\alpha({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).{\vec{u}}_{1}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})).{\vec{u}}_{2}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})\over h}\quad\in\mathbb{R}.

(8.20)

With the natural canonical isomorphism
{\cal L}(E;{E^{*}})\simeq{\cal L}(E,E;\mathbb{R})
, cf. (
T.16
) with
{E^{**}}\simeq E
,
we can write
d\alpha({p_{\!{\cal E}}})({\vec{u}}_{1}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})).{\vec{u}}_{2}({p_{\!{\cal E}}})=d\alpha({p_{\!{\cal E}}})({\vec{u}}_{1}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}),{\vec{u}}_{2}({p_{\!{\cal E}}}))
, i.e.

d\alpha({\vec{u}}_{1}).{\vec{u}}_{2}=d\alpha({\vec{u}}_{1},{\vec{u}}_{2}).

(8.21)

Thus the push-forward
\Psi_{*}(d\alpha){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}(d\alpha)_{*}
of
d\alpha
, is given by, for all
{{\vec{w}}_{1}},{{\vec{w}}_{2}}\in\Gamma({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}})
,
in short,

(d\alpha)_{*}({{\vec{w}}_{1}},{{\vec{w}}_{2}})=d\alpha({{\vec{w}}_{1}}^{*},{{\vec{w}}_{2}}^{*}),

(8.22)

i.e., with
{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
,
(d\alpha)_{*}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).{{\vec{w}}_{1}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})).{{\vec{w}}_{2}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})=(d\alpha({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).d\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).{{\vec{w}}_{1}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})).d\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).{{\vec{w}}_{2}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})
.

In particular,

(d^{2}f)_{*}({{\vec{w}}_{1}},{{\vec{w}}_{2}})=d^{2}f(d\Psi^{-1}.{{\vec{w}}_{1}},d\Psi^{-1}.{{\vec{w}}_{2}})
(
=d^{2}f({{\vec{w}}_{1}}^{*},{{\vec{w}}_{2}}^{*})
).

8.7
\Psi_{*}(d\alpha)
versus
d(\Psi_{*}\alpha)
: No commutativity

Here
\Psi
is
C^{2}
,
{\vec{u}}\in\Gamma({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}})
,
{p_{\!{\cal E}}}\in{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}
and
{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Psi({p_{\!{\cal E}}})
.
We have

\Psi_{*}\alpha({p_{\!{\cal F}}})=\alpha({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).d\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})=\alpha(\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})).d\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})
, thus, for all
{{\vec{w}}_{1}}\in\Gamma({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}})
,

d(\psi_{*}\alpha)({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).{{\vec{w}}_{1}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})=(d\alpha({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).d\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).{{\vec{w}}_{1}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})).d\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})+\alpha({p_{\!{\cal E}}}).d^{2}\Psi^{-1}({p_{\!{\cal F}}}).{{\vec{w}}_{1}}({p_{\!{\cal F}}})\;\in{F^{*}},

(8.23)

thus, for all
{{\vec{w}}_{1}},{{\vec{w}}_{2}}\in\Gamma({{\cal U}_{\!{\cal F}}})
, in short

d(\psi_{*}\alpha)({{\vec{w}}_{1}},{{\vec{w}}_{2}})=d\alpha(d\Psi^{-1}.{{\vec{w}}_{1}},d\Psi^{-1}.{{\vec{w}}_{2}})+\alpha.d^{2}\Psi^{-1}({{\vec{w}}_{1}},{{\vec{w}}_{2}})\neq d\alpha({{\vec{w}}_{1}}^{*},{{\vec{w}}_{2}}^{*})\quad\hbox{in general}.

(8.24)

So the differentiation
d
and the push-forward
∗
do not commute (
d(\Psi_{*}\alpha)=\Psi_{*}(d\alpha)
iff
\Psi
is affine).

Part III
Lie derivative

9
Lie derivative

9.0
Purpose and first results

9.0.1
Purpose?

Cauchy’s approach may be insufficient, e.g.:

1.

- Cauchy’s approach aims to compare two vectors deformed by a motion, thanks to a Euclidean dot product and the deformation gradient
F
, with the deformation tensor
C
defined by
(C.{\vec{W}}_{1})\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{\vec{W}}_{2}:=(F.{\vec{W}}_{1})\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}(F.{\vec{W}}_{2})
.
It is a quantitative approach (needs a chosen Euclidean dot product: foot? metre?).

- Cauchy’s approach is a first order method (dedicated to linear material): Only the first order Taylor expansion of the motion is used: Only
d\Phi=F
is used (the “slope”), not
d^{2}\Phi=dF
(the “curvature”) or higher derivatives.

2.

- Lie’s approach aims to build qualitative “covariant objective constitutive laws” (some will be discredited
afterward
, because of invariance or thermodynamical requirements).

- Lie’s approach “naturally” applies to non-linear materials thanks to second order Lie derivatives which uses the second order Taylor expansion of the motion.

- In a non planar surface
S
, you need the Lie derivative if you want to derive along a trajectory.

- In a Galilean Euclidean framework (quantification), the first order Lie derivatives approach give the same results than Cauchy’s approach.

(Cauchy died in 1857, and Lie was born in 1842: Unfortunately Cauchy could not use the Lie derivative.)

9.0.2
Basic results

The Eulerian velocity of the motion is
{\vec{v}}
. With the material derivative is
{D{{\cal E}\!ul}\over Dt}:={\partial{{\cal E}\!ul}\over\partial t}+d{{\cal E}\!ul}.{\vec{v}}
:

1.

The Lie derivative
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}f
of a Eulerian scalar valued function
f
is the material derivative

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}f={Df\over Dt}.

(9.1)

2.

The Lie derivative
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}
of a (Eulerian) vector field
{\vec{w}}
is more than just the material derivative
{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}
:

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}={D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}-d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}.

(9.2)

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}
gives the rate of stress on
{\vec{w}}
due to a flow, and in particular
the
-d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}
term in
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}
tells that the spatial variations of
{\vec{v}}
(variations of the flow) act on the evolution of the stress (anticipated).

3.

(
9.1
)-(
9.2
) enable to define the Lie derivatives of tensors of any order.

9.1
Definition

9.1.1
Issue (ubiquity gift)…

{\widetilde{\Phi}}
is supposed to be regular.

{\vec{v}}(t,p(t))={\partial{\widetilde{\Phi}}\over\partial t}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
is the Eulerian velocity at
t
at
p(t)={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
.
Recall: If
{{\cal E}\!ul}
is a Eulerian function then its material time derivative is

{D{{\cal E}\!ul}\over Dt}(t,p(t))=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{{{\cal E}\!ul}({t{+}h},p({t{+}h}))-{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,p(t))\over h}.

(9.3)

Issue: The rate
{{{\cal E}\!ul}({t{+}h},p({t{+}h}))-{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,p(t))\over h}
raises questions:

1- The difference
{{\cal E}\!ul}({t{+}h},p({t{+}h}))-{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,p(t))
requires the time and space ubiquity gift to be calculated by an observer,
since it mixes two distinct times,
t
and
{t{+}h}
, and two distinct locations,
p(t)
and
{p(t{+}h)}
.

2- The difference
{{\cal E}\!ul}({t{+}h},p({t{+}h}))-{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,p(t))
can be impossible: E.g. if
{{\cal E}\!ul}={\vec{w}}
is a vector field in a “non planar surface considered on its own” (manifold)
then
{{\cal E}\!ul}({t{+}h},p({t{+}h}))
and
{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,p(t))
don’t belong to the same (tangent) vector space (so the difference
{\vec{w}}({t{+}h},p({t{+}h}))-{\vec{w}}(t,p(t))
is meaningless).

9.1.2
…Toward a solution (without ubiquity gift)…

To compare
{{\cal E}\!ul}({t{+}h},p({t{+}h}))
and
{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,p(t))
(to get the evolution of
{{\cal E}\!ul}
along a trajectory),
you need the duration
h
to get from
t
to
{t{+}h}

and to move from
p(t)
to
{p(t{+}h)}
.
So, you must:

\bullet
take the value
{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,{p_{t}}))
with you (for memory),

\bullet
move along the considered trajectory, and doing so, the value
{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,{p_{t}})
has possibly changed to, with
\tau={t{+}h}
,

(({\Phi^{t}_{\tau}})_{*}{{\cal E}\!ul}_{t})({p_{\tau}}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{{\cal E}\!ul}_{t*}(\tau,{p_{\tau}})\quad(\hbox{push-forward});

(9.4)

\bullet
And now, at
(\tau,{p_{\tau}})
where you are, you can compare the actual value
{{\cal E}\!ul}(\tau,{p_{\tau}})

with the value
{{\cal E}\!ul}_{t*}(\tau,{p_{\tau}})
you arrived with (the transported memory), thus the difference

{{\cal E}\!ul}(\tau,{p_{\tau}})-{{\cal E}\!ul}_{t*}(\tau,{p_{\tau}})

(9.5)

is meaningful for a human being
since it is computed at a unique time
\tau
and at a unique point
{p_{\tau}}
(no gift of ubiquity required).

Figure 9.1:
To compute (
9.5
) with
{{\cal E}\!ul}={\vec{w}}
a (Eulerian) vector field:
At
t
define the vector field
{\vec{w}}_{t}
in
{\Omega_{t}}
by
{\vec{w}}_{t}({p_{t}}):={\vec{w}}(t,{p_{t}})
.
The (spatial) curve
c_{t}:s\rightarrow{p_{t}}=c_{t}(s)
in
{\Omega_{t}}
is an integral curve of
{\vec{w}}_{t}
, i.e. satisfies
c_{t}{}^{\prime}(s)={\vec{w}}_{t}(c_{t}(s))
.
c_{t}
is transformed by
{\Phi^{t}_{\tau}}
into the (spatial) curve
c_{\tau}={\Phi^{t}_{\tau}}\circ c_{t}:s\rightarrow{p_{\tau}}=c_{\tau}(s){=}{\Phi^{t}_{\tau}}(c_{t}(s))
in
{\Omega_{\tau}}
;
Hence
c_{\tau}{}^{\prime}(s)=d{\Phi^{t}_{\tau}}({p_{t}}).c^{\prime}(s)=d{\Phi^{t}_{\tau}}({p_{t}}).{\vec{w}}_{t}({p_{t}}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{w}}_{t*}(\tau,{p_{\tau}})
is the tangent vector at
c_{\tau}
at
{p_{\tau}}
(push-forward).
Thus the difference
{\vec{w}}(\tau,{p_{\tau}})-{\vec{w}}_{t*}(\tau,{p_{\tau}})
can be computed by a human being,
i.e. without ubiquity gift.

9.1.3
… The Lie derivative, first definition

Definition 9.1

The Lie derivative
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{{\cal E}\!ul}
along
{\vec{v}}
of an Eulerian function
{{\cal E}\!ul}

is the Eulerian function
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{{\cal E}\!ul}
defined by, at
t
at
{p_{t}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
,

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,{p_{t}}):=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{{{\cal E}\!ul}({t{+}h},{p(t{+}h)})-({\Phi^{t}_{t{+}h}})_{*}{{\cal E}\!ul}_{t}({p(t{+}h)})\over h}.

(9.6)

Interpretation:

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{{\cal E}\!ul}
measures the rate of change of
{{\cal E}\!ul}
along a trajectory:

\bullet

{{\cal E}\!ul}({t{+}h},{p(t{+}h)})
is the value of
{{\cal E}\!ul}
at
{t{+}h}
at
{p(t{+}h)}
, see figure
9.1
.

\bullet

{{\cal E}\!ul}_{t*}({t{+}h},{p(t{+}h)})=(({\Phi^{t}_{t{+}h}})_{*}{{\cal E}\!ul}_{t})({t{+}h},{p(t{+}h)})
is exclusively strain related:
It is the memory transported along a flow, i.e. the value
{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,{p_{t}})
distorted by the flow.

So, with
g
defined by
g(\tau)=((\Phi^{t}_{\tau})_{*}{{\cal E}\!ul}_{t})({p_{\tau}})

(in particular
g(t)={{\cal E}\!ul}_{t}({p_{t}})
):

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,{p_{t}}):=g^{\prime}(t)=\lim_{\tau\rightarrow t}{g(\tau)-g(t)\over\tau-t}\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\scriptsize also written}}\quad{d((\Phi^{t}_{t{+}h})_{*}{{\cal E}\!ul}_{t})({p(t{+}h)})\over dt}_{|\tau=t}.

(9.7)

9.1.4
A more general definition

The rate in (
9.6
) has to be slightly modified to be adequate in all situations:

{{\cal E}\!ul}({t{+}h},{p(t{+}h)})-{{\cal E}\!ul}_{*}({t{+}h},{p(t{+}h)})
is computed at
({t{+}h},{p(t{+}h)})
which moves as
h\rightarrow 0
,
and on a “non-planar manifold” this is problematic.
The “natural” definition is to arrive with the memory:

Definition 9.2

The Lie derivative
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{{\cal E}\!ul}
along
{\vec{v}}
of an Eulerian function
{{\cal E}\!ul}

is the Eulerian function
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{{\cal E}\!ul}
defined by, at
t
at
{p_{t}}={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}(t)
,

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,{p_{t}}):=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,{p_{t}})-({\Phi^{t{-}h}_{t}})_{*}{{\cal E}\!ul}_{t{-}h}({p_{t}})\over h}.

(9.8)

I.e. with
\widetilde{g}
defined by
\widetilde{g}(\tau)=((\Phi^{\tau}_{t})_{*}{{\cal E}\!ul}_{\tau})({p_{t}})
(in particular
\widetilde{g}(t)={{\cal E}\!ul}(t,{p_{t}})
):

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,{p_{t}}):=\widetilde{g}^{\prime}(t)=\lim_{\tau\rightarrow t}{\widetilde{g}(t)-\widetilde{g}(\tau)\over t-\tau}=\lim_{\tau\rightarrow t}{\widetilde{g}(\tau)-\widetilde{g}(t)\over\tau-t}\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\scriptsize also written}}\quad{d((\Phi^{\tau}_{t})_{*}{{\cal E}\!ul}_{\tau})({p_{t}})\over d\tau}_{|\tau=t}.

(9.9)

Here the observer must:

\bullet
At
{t{-}h}
at
{p({t{-}h})}={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}({t{-}h})
, take the value
{{\cal E}\!ul}({t{-}h},{p({t{-}h})})
,

\bullet
travel along the trajectory
{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}
,

\bullet
once at
t
at
{p_{t}}={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}(t)
, this value has become
(({\Phi^{t}_{t{-}h}})_{*}{{\cal E}\!ul}_{t{-}h})({p_{t}})
(transported memory),

\bullet
and then the comparison with
{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,{p_{t}})
can be done in
{\Omega_{t}}
(no ubiquity gift required).

Exercice 9.3

Prove: (
9.6
) and (
9.8
) are equivalent.

Answer
. With
({\Phi^{t}_{t{+}h}})^{*}.({\Phi^{t}_{t{+}h}})_{*}=I
,
(
9.6
) gives

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,{p_{t}})=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{({\Phi^{t}_{t{+}h}})^{*}{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,{p_{t}})-{{\cal E}\!ul}_{t}(t,{p_{t}})\over h}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{({\Phi^{t}_{t{-}h}})^{*}{{\cal E}\!ul}_{t{-}h})({p_{t}})-{{\cal E}\!ul}_{t}({p_{t}})\over-h}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{{{\cal E}\!ul}_{t}({p_{t}})-(({\Phi^{t}_{t{-}h}})^{*}{{\cal E}\!ul}_{t{-}h})({p_{t}})\over h},

and
({\Phi^{t}_{t{-}h}})^{*}=({\Phi^{t{-}h}_{t}})_{*}
.

9.1.5
Equivalent definition (differential geometry)

Definition 9.4

The Lie derivative of a Eulerian function
{{\cal E}\!ul}
along a flow of Eulerian velocity
{\vec{v}}

is the Eulerian function
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{{\cal E}\!ul}
defined at
(t,{p_{t}})
by

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,{p_{t}}):=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{(({\Phi^{t}_{t{+}h}})^{*}{{\cal E}\!ul}_{t{+}h})({p_{t}})-{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,{p_{t}})\over h},\quad\hbox{rate in\penalty\ ${\Omega_{t}}$}.

(9.10)

In other words, if
\hat{g}
is defined by
\hat{g}(\tau)=((\Phi^{t}_{\tau})^{*}{{\cal E}\!ul}_{\tau})({p_{t}})

(in particular
\hat{g}(t)={{\cal E}\!ul}(t,{p_{t}})
), then

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,{p_{t}}):=\hat{g}^{\prime}(t)=\lim_{\tau\rightarrow t}{\hat{g}(\tau)-\hat{g}(t)\over\tau-t}\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\scriptsize also written}}\quad{d((\Phi^{t}_{\tau})^{*}{{\cal E}\!ul}_{\tau})({p_{t}})\over d\tau}_{|\tau=t}.

(9.11)

Exercice 9.5

Prove: (
9.8
) and (
9.10
) are equivalent.

Answer
. (
9.10
) also reads

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,{p_{t}})=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{(({\Phi^{t}_{t{-}h}})^{*}{{\cal E}\!ul}_{t{-}h})({p_{t}})-{{\cal E}\!ul}_{t}({p_{t}})\over-h}
,
and
({\Phi^{t}_{t{-}h}})^{*}.({\Phi^{t{-}h}_{t}})_{*}=I
.

Remark 9.6

More precise definition, as in (
2.3
): E.g. with (
9.10
),
the Lie derivative
\widetilde{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{{\cal E}\!ul}
of a Eulerian function
\widetilde{{\cal E}\!ul}
along a flow of Eulerian velocity
{\vec{v}}
is the Eulerian function defined by, at
t
at
{p_{t}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
,

\widetilde{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,{p_{t}}):=((t,{p_{t}}),{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,{p_{t}})\quad(\hbox{pointed function at $(t,{p_{t}})$}),

(9.12)

And, to lighten the notation,
\widetilde{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,{p_{t}}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,{p_{t}})
(second component of
\widetilde{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{{\cal E}\!ul}(t,{p_{t}})
).

9.2
Lie derivative of a scalar function

Let
f
be a
C^{1}
Eulerian scalar valued function. With
({\Phi^{t{-}h}_{t}})_{*}f_{t{-}h}({p_{t}})=f_{t{-}h}({p({t{-}h})})
, cf. (
6.10
), we get

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}f(t,{p_{t}})\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdefDLrn2})}}\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{f(t,{p_{t}})-f({t{-}h},p({t{-}h}))\over h},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}f={Df\over Dt}$}={\partial f\over\partial t}+df.{\vec{v}}.

(9.13)

So, for scalar functions, the Lie derivative is the material derivative.

Interpretation:

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}f
measures the rate of change of
f
along a trajectory.

Proposition 9.7

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}f=0
iff
f
is constant along any trajectory (the real value is the memory value):

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}f=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\forall t,\tau\in[{t_{0}},T],\;({\Phi^{t}_{\tau}}_{*})f_{t}({p_{\tau}})=f(t,p(t))\;\;\hbox{when}\;\;{p_{\tau}}={\Phi^{t}_{\tau}}({p_{t}}),

(9.14)

i.e. iff
f(t,p(t))=f({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})
when
p(t)={\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})
,
i.e. iff
f
let itself be carried by the flow (unchanged).

Proof.
Let
p(t)={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})={p_{t}}
for all
t
, so
p(\tau)={\widetilde{\Phi}}(\tau,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})={p_{\tau}}={\Phi^{t}_{t{+}h}}({p_{t}})=\Phi^{t}(\tau,{p_{t}})
.

\Leftarrow
:
If
f_{\tau}=({\Phi^{t}_{t{+}h}})_{*}f_{t}
, then
f_{\tau}({p_{\tau}})=f_{t}({p_{t}})
,
thus
\lim_{\tau\rightarrow t}{f(\tau,p(\tau))-f(t,p(t))\over\tau-t}=0
, that is,
{Df\over Dt}=0
.

\Rightarrow
:
If
{Df\over Dt}=0
then
f(t,p(t))
is a constant function on the trajectory
t\rightarrow{\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
,
for any particle
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
,
so
f(\tau,p(\tau))=f(t,{p_{t}})
when
p(\tau)={\Phi^{t}_{t{+}h}}({p_{t}})
, that is,
f(\tau,{p_{\tau}})=({\Phi^{t}_{t{+}h}})_{*}f_{t}({p_{\tau}})
.

Exercice 9.8

Prove:

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}f)={D^{2}f\over Dt^{2}}={\partial^{2}f\over\partial t^{2}}+2d({\partial f\over\partial t}).{\vec{v}}+d^{2}f({\vec{v}},{\vec{v}})+df.({\partial{\vec{v}}\over\partial t}+d{\vec{v}}).

Answer
. See (
2.23
).

9.3
Lie derivative of a vector field

9.3.1
Formula

Let
{\vec{w}}
be a
C^{1}
(Eulerian) vector field (interpreted as an “internal force field” in the following).

Proposition 9.9

\framebox{$\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}={D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}-d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}$}={\partial{\vec{w}}\over\partial t}+d{\vec{w}}.{\vec{v}}-d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}.

(9.15)

So the Lie derivative is not reduced to the material derivative
{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}
(unless
d{\vec{v}}=0
, i.e. unless
{\vec{v}}
is uniform):
The spatial variations
d{\vec{v}}
of
{\vec{v}}
influences the rate of stress:
{\vec{v}}
tries to bend
{\vec{w}}
(which is expected).

Proof.

Let
{\vec{g}}:\tau\rightarrow{\vec{g}}(\tau)=({\Phi^{t*}_{\tau}}{\vec{w}})(t,p(t))=d{\Phi^{t}_{\tau}}({p_{t}})^{-1}.{\vec{w}}(\tau,p(\tau))
when
p(\tau)=\Phi^{t}(\tau,{p_{t}})
, so (
9.10
) reads
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}(t,{p_{t}})={{\vec{g}}\,^{\prime}}(t)
.
With
{\vec{z}}(\tau):={\vec{w}}(\tau,p(\tau))=d{\Phi^{t}}(\tau,{p_{t}}).{\vec{g}}(\tau)
,

\displaystyle{{\vec{z}}\,^{\prime}(\tau)={D{\vec{w}}\over D\tau}(\tau,p(\tau))=}

\displaystyle{{}{\partial(d{\Phi^{t}})\over\partial\tau}(\tau,{p_{t}}).{\vec{g}}(\tau)+d{\Phi^{t}}(\tau,{p_{t}}).{{\vec{g}}\,^{\prime}}(\tau)}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}(d{\vec{v}}(\tau,p(\tau)).{F^{t}}(\tau,{p_{t}})).({F^{t}}(\tau,{p_{t}})^{-1}.{\vec{w}}(\tau,p(\tau)))+F^{t}_{\tau}({p_{t}}).{{\vec{g}}\,^{\prime}}(\tau)}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}d{\vec{v}}(\tau,p(\tau)).{\vec{w}}(\tau,p(\tau))+F^{t}_{\tau}({p_{t}}).{{\vec{g}}\,^{\prime}}(\tau).}

(9.16)

Thus
{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}(t,{p_{t}})=d{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}}).{\vec{w}}(t,{p_{t}})+I.{{\vec{g}}\,^{\prime}}(t)
,
thus
{{\vec{g}}\,^{\prime}}(t)={D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}(t,{p_{t}})-d{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}}).{\vec{w}}(t,{p_{t}})
.

Quantification:

Basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
,

{\vec{v}}=\sum_{i}v_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,
{\vec{w}}=\sum_{i}w_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,

d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{e}}_{j}=\sum_{ij}v_{i|j}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,

d{\vec{w}}.{\vec{e}}_{j}=\sum_{ij}w_{i|j}{\vec{e}}_{i}
; Then

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\partial w_{i}\over\partial t}{\vec{e}}_{i}+{\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}w_{i|j}v_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}-{\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}v_{i|j}w_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}.

(9.17)

So (column matrix), with
[\cdot]:=[\cdot]_{|{\vec{e}}}
,

\framebox{$\displaystyle[{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}]=[{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}]-[d{\vec{v}}].[{\vec{w}}]$}\quad(=[{\partial{\vec{w}}\over\partial t}]+[d{\vec{w}}.{\vec{v}}]-[d{\vec{v}}].[{\vec{w}}]).

(9.18)

(And
[d{\vec{w}}.{\vec{v}}]=[d{\vec{w}}].[{\vec{v}}]
.)
Duality notations:

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}=\sum_{i}{\partial w^{i}\over\partial t}{\vec{e}}_{i}+\sum_{ij}w^{i}_{|j}v^{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}-\sum_{ij}v^{i}_{|j}w^{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}
.

9.3.2
Interpretation: Flow resistance measurement

Proposition 9.10

{\Phi^{t_{0}}}
is supposed to be a
C^{2}
motion and a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism in space,
and
{\vec{w}}
is a vector field.

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\forall t\in[{t_{0}},T],\;\;{\vec{w}}_{t}=({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})_{*}{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}.

(9.19)

i.e.,
{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}=d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}

\Leftrightarrow

the actual vector
{\vec{w}}(t,p(t))
is equal to
{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}})={\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}(t,p(t))
the deformed vector by the flow, see figure
9.1
.
So: The Lie derivative
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}
vanishes
iff
{\vec{w}}
does not resist the flow (let itself be deformed by the flow), i.e. iff
{\vec{w}}(t,{p_{t}})={\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}(t,{p_{t}})
.

Proof.
We have
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}={D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}-d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}
and
{\partial{F^{t_{0}}}\over\partial t}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})=d{\vec{v}}(t,p(t)).{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})
, cf. (
3.33
).

\Leftarrow
(derivation):
Suppose
{\vec{w}}(t,p(t))={F^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{w}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})
when
p(t)={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})
.
Then
{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}(t,p(t))={\partial{F^{t_{0}}}\over\partial t}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{w}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})=(d{\vec{v}}(t,p(t)).{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})).({F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})^{-1}.{\vec{w}}(t,p(t)))=d{\vec{v}}(t,p(t)).{\vec{w}}(t,p(t))
,
thus
{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}-d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}=0
. (See proposition
3.14
.)

\Rightarrow
(integration):
Suppose
{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}=d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}
.
Let
{\vec{f}}(t)=({F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}))^{-1}.{\vec{w}}(t,p(t))
(= pull-back
({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{*}{\vec{w}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})
) when
p(t)={\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})
;
So
{\vec{w}}(t,p(t))={F^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{f}}(t)

and
{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}(t,p(t))={\partial{F^{t_{0}}}\over\partial t}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{f}}(t)+{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{f}}\,^{\prime}(t)=d{\vec{v}}(t,p(t)).{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{f}}(t)+{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{f}}\,^{\prime}(t)=d{\vec{v}}(t,p(t)).{\vec{w}}(t,p(t))+{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{f}}\,^{\prime}(t)\mathop{=}^{hyp.}{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}(t,p(t))+{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{f}}\,^{\prime}(t)
for all
t
;
Thus
{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{f}}\,^{\prime}(t)=\vec{0}
, thus
{\vec{f}}\,^{\prime}(t)=\vec{0}
(because
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
is a diffeomorphism),
thus
{\vec{f}}(t)={\vec{f}}({t_{0}})
,
i.e.
{\vec{w}}_{t}=({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})_{*}{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}
, for all
t
.

9.3.3
Autonomous Lie derivative and Lie bracket

The Lie bracket of two vector fields
{\vec{v}}
and
{\vec{w}}
is

[{\vec{v}},{\vec{w}}]:=d{\vec{w}}.{\vec{v}}-d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\cal L}^{0}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}.

(9.20)

And
{\cal L}^{0}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}=[{\vec{v}},{\vec{w}}]
is called the autonomous Lie derivative of
{\vec{w}}
along
{\vec{v}}
.
Thus

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}={\partial{\vec{w}}\over\partial t}+[{\vec{v}},{\vec{w}}]={\partial{\vec{w}}\over\partial t}+{\cal L}^{0}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}.

(9.21)

NB:
{\cal L}^{0}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}
is used when
{\vec{v}}
et
{\vec{w}}
are stationary vector fields,
thus does not concern objectivity: A stationary vector field in a referential
is not necessary stationary in another (moving) referential.

9.4
Examples

9.4.1
Lie Derivative of a vector field along itself

(
9.15
) with
{\vec{w}}={\vec{v}}
gives

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{v}}={\partial{\vec{v}}\over\partial t}.

In particular, if
{\vec{v}}
is a stationary vector field then

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{v}}=\vec{0}
(
=[{\vec{v}},{\vec{v}}]
).

9.4.2
Lie derivative along a uniform flow

Here
d{\vec{v}}=0
, thus

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}={D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}={\partial{\vec{w}}\over\partial t}+d{\vec{w}}.{\vec{v}}\quad(\hbox{when $d{\vec{v}}=0$}).

(9.22)

Here the flow is rectilinear (
d{\vec{v}}=0
): there is no curvature (of the flow)
to influence the stress on
{\vec{w}}
.

Moreover, if
{\vec{w}}
is stationary, that is
{\partial{\vec{w}}\over\partial t}=0
, then

{\cal L}_{{\vec{v}}}{\vec{w}}=d{\vec{w}}.{\vec{v}}

= the directional derivative
{\partial{\vec{w}}\over\partial{\vec{v}}}
of the vector field
{\vec{w}}
in the direction
{\vec{v}}
.

9.4.3
Lie derivative of a uniform vector field

Here
d{\vec{w}}(t,p)=0
, thus

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}={\partial{\vec{w}}\over\partial t}-d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}\quad(\hbox{when $d{\vec{w}}=0$}),

(9.23)

thus the stress on
{\vec{w}}
is due to the space variations of
{\vec{v}}
. Moreover, is
{\vec{w}}
is stationary then

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}=-d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}.

9.4.4
Uniaxial stretch of an elastic material

\bullet
Strain. With
[\overrightarrow{OP}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[{\vec{X}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{X\cr Y}
,
with
\xi>0
,
t\geq{t_{0}}
,
p(t)={\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,P)
and
[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[\overrightarrow{Op(t)}]_{|{\vec{e}}}
:

[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{x\cr y}=\pmatrix{X\cr Y}+\xi(t{-}{t_{0}})\pmatrix{X\cr 0}=\pmatrix{X(1+\xi(t{-}{t_{0}}))\cr Y\hfill}.

(9.24)

\bullet
Eulerian velocity
{\vec{v}}(t,p)=\pmatrix{\xi X\cr 0\hfill}=\pmatrix{{\xi\over 1+\xi(t{-}{t_{0}})}x\cr 0\hfill}
,

d{\vec{v}}(t,p)=\pmatrix{{\xi\over 1+\xi(t{-}{t_{0}})}&0\cr 0&0}
(independent of
p
).

\bullet
Deformation gradient (independent of
P
), with
\kappa_{t}=\xi(t{-}{t_{0}})
:

F_{t}=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)=\pmatrix{1+\kappa_{t}&0\cr 0&1}=I+\kappa_{t}\pmatrix{1&0\cr 0&0}.

(9.25)

Infinitesimal strain tensor, with
F_{t}^{T}=F_{t}
here:

{{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}}(P)=F_{t}-I=\kappa_{t}\pmatrix{1&0\cr 0&0}={\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}_{t}.

(9.26)

\bullet
Stress. Constitutive law = Linear isotropic elasticity:

{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}_{t}({p_{t}})=\lambda{\rm Tr}({\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}_{t})I+2\mu{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}_{t}=\kappa_{t}\pmatrix{\lambda{+}2\mu&0\cr 0&\lambda}={\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}_{t}.

(9.27)

Cauchy stress vector
\vec{T}
on a surface at
p
with normal
{\vec{n}}_{t}(p)=\pmatrix{n^{1}\cr n^{2}}={\vec{n}}
:

\vec{T}_{t}({p_{t}})={\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}_{t}.{\vec{n}}=\kappa_{t}\pmatrix{(\lambda{+}2\mu)n_{1}\cr\lambda n_{2}}=\xi(t{-}{t_{0}})\pmatrix{(\lambda{+}2\mu)n_{1}\cr\lambda n_{2}}=\vec{T}_{t}.

(9.28)

\bullet
Push-forwards:

\vec{T}_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}})=0
,
thus
{F^{t_{0}}_{{t_{0}}{+}h}}({p_{t_{0}}}).\vec{T}_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}})=\vec{0}
.

\bullet
Lie derivative:

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\vec{T}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})=\lim_{t\rightarrow{t_{0}}}{\vec{T}_{t}({p_{t}})-{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).\vec{T}_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}})\over t-{t_{0}}}=\xi\pmatrix{(\lambda{+}2\mu)n_{1}\cr\lambda n_{2}}\quad\hbox{(rate of stress at $({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})$)}.

(9.29)

\bullet
Generic computation with

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\vec{T}={\partial\vec{T}\over\partial t}+d\vec{T}.{\vec{v}}-d{\vec{v}}.\vec{T}
:
(
9.28
) gives

{\partial\vec{T}\over\partial t}=\xi\pmatrix{(\lambda{+}2\mu)\,n^{1}\cr\lambda\,n^{2}}

and

d\vec{T}=0

and

d{\vec{v}}_{t}.\vec{T}_{t}=\pmatrix{{\xi\over 1+\xi(t{-}{t_{0}})}&0\cr 0&0}.\xi(t{-}{t_{0}})\pmatrix{(\lambda{+}2\mu)\,n^{1}\cr\lambda\,n^{2}}={\xi^{2}(t{-}{t_{0}})\over 1+\xi(t{-}{t_{0}})}\pmatrix{(\lambda{+}2\mu)\,n^{1}\cr 0}
.
In particular,

d{\vec{v}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}}).\vec{T}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})=\vec{0}
. Thus

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\vec{T}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})=\xi\pmatrix{(\lambda{+}2\mu)\,n^{1}\cr\lambda\,n^{2}}=
rate of stress at the initial
({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})
.

9.4.5
Simple shear of an elastic material

Euclidean basis
({\vec{e}}_{1},{\vec{e}}_{2})
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the same basis at any time.
Initial configuration
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}=[0,L_{1}]\otimes[0,L_{2}]
.
Initial position

[\overrightarrow{OP}]_{{\vec{e}}}=[\overrightarrow{O{p_{t_{0}}}}]_{{\vec{e}}}=[{\vec{X}}]_{{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{X\cr Y}
.
Let
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
,
{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})
,
[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[\overrightarrow{Op(t)}]_{|{\vec{e}}}
, and

[{\vec{x}}]_{{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{x=\varphi^{1}(t,X,Y)=X\cr y=\varphi^{2}(t,X,Y)\hfill}=\pmatrix{X+\xi(t{-}{t_{0}})Y\cr Y\hfill}.

(9.30)

\bullet
Eulerian velocity
{\vec{v}}_{t}({p_{t}})=\pmatrix{\xi Y\cr 0\hfill}=\pmatrix{\xi y\cr 0\hfill}
, thus
d{\vec{v}}_{t}({p_{t}})=\pmatrix{0&\xi\cr 0&0}
.

\bullet
Strain. With
\kappa_{t}=\xi(t{-}{t_{0}})
, deformation gradient (independent of
P
):

d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)=\pmatrix{1&\kappa_{t}\cr 0&1}={F^{t_{0}}_{t}},\quad\hbox{thus}\quad{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}-I=\kappa_{t}\pmatrix{0&1\cr 0&0}.

(9.31)

\bullet
Infinitesimal strain tensor:

{{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}}(P)={{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P){-}I+({F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P){-}I)^{T}\over 2}={\kappa_{t}\over 2}\pmatrix{0&1\cr 1&0}={\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}_{t}.

(9.32)

\bullet
Stress. Constitutive law, usual linear isotropic elasticity (requires a Euclidean dot product):

{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}(t,{p_{t}})=\lambda{\rm Tr}({\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}_{t})I+2\mu{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}_{t}=\mu\kappa_{t}\pmatrix{0&1\cr 1&0}={\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}_{t}.

(9.33)

Cauchy stress vector
\vec{T}(t,{p_{t}})
(at
t
at
{p_{t}}
) on a surface at
p
with normal

{\vec{n}}_{t}(p)=\pmatrix{n^{1}\cr n^{2}}={\vec{n}}
:

\vec{T}_{t}={\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}_{t}.{\vec{n}}=\mu\kappa_{t}\pmatrix{\,n^{2}\cr\,n^{1}}=\mu\xi(t{-}{t_{0}})\pmatrix{\,n^{2}\cr\,n^{1}}=\vec{T}(t)\quad(\hbox{stress independent of\penalty\ ${p_{t}}$}).

(9.34)

\bullet
Lie derivative, with
\vec{T}_{t_{0}}=\vec{0}
:

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\vec{T}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})=\lim_{t\rightarrow{t_{0}}}{\vec{T}_{t}({p_{t}})-{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).\vec{T}_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}})\over t-{t_{0}}}=\mu\xi\pmatrix{\,n^{2}\cr\,n^{1}}\quad\hbox{(rate of stress at $({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})$)}.

(9.35)

\bullet
Generic computation:
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\vec{T}={\partial\vec{T}\over\partial t}+d\vec{T}.{\vec{v}}-d{\vec{v}}.\vec{T}
.
(
9.34
) gives

{\partial\vec{T}\over\partial t}(t,p)=\mu\xi\pmatrix{\,n^{2}\cr\,n^{1}}
and
d\vec{T}=0
.
With
d{\vec{v}}_{t_{0}}.\vec{T}_{t_{0}}=\vec{0}
.
Thus

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\vec{T}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})=\mu\xi\pmatrix{\,n^{2}\cr\,n^{1}}
.

9.4.6
Shear flow

Stationary shear field, see (
5.11
) with
\alpha=0
and
{t_{0}}=0
:

{\vec{v}}(x,y)=\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&v^{1}(x,y)=\lambda y,\cr&v^{2}(x,y)=0,\cr}}\,\right.\qquad d{\vec{v}}(x,y)=\pmatrix{0&\lambda\cr 0&0}.

(9.36)

Let
{\vec{w}}(t,p)=\pmatrix{0\cr b}={\vec{w}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})
(constant in time and uniform in space).
Then
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}=-d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}=\pmatrix{-\lambda b\cr 0}
measures “the resistance to deformation due to the flow”. See figure
9.2
,
the virtual vector
{\vec{w}}_{*}(t,p)=d\Phi({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{w}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})
being the vector
that would have let itself be carried by the flow (the push-forward).

Figure 9.2:
Shear flow, cf. (
9.36
), with
{\vec{w}}
constant and uniform.
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}
measures the resistance to the deformation.

9.4.7
Spin

Rotating flow: Continuing (
5.14
):

{\vec{v}}(x,y)=\omega\pmatrix{0&-1\cr 1&0}\pmatrix{x\cr y},\quad d{\vec{v}}(x,y)=\omega\pmatrix{0&-1\cr 1&0}=\omega\,Rot(\pi/2).

(9.37)

In particular
d^{2}{\vec{v}}=0
. With
{\vec{w}}={\vec{w}}_{0}
constant and uniform we get

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}_{0}=-d{\vec{v}}(p).{\vec{w}}_{0}=-\omega\,Rot(\pi/2).{\vec{w}}_{0}\qquad(\perp\;\pmatrix{a\cr b}={\vec{w}}_{0}).

(9.38)

gives “the force at which
{\vec{w}}
refuses to turn with the flow”.

9.4.8
Second order Lie derivative

Exercice 9.11

Let
{\vec{v}},{\vec{w}}
be
C^{2}
. Prove:

\displaystyle{{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}})=}

\displaystyle{{}{D^{2}{\vec{w}}\over Dt^{2}}-2d{\vec{v}}.{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}-{D(d{\vec{v}})\over Dt}.{\vec{w}}+d{\vec{v}}.d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}},}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}{\partial^{2}{\vec{w}}\over\partial t^{2}}+2d{\partial{\vec{w}}\over\partial t}.{\vec{v}}-2d{\vec{v}}.{\partial{\vec{w}}\over\partial t}+d{\vec{w}}.{\partial{\vec{v}}\over\partial t}-d{\partial{\vec{v}}\over\partial t}.{\vec{w}}}

\displaystyle{{}\qquad+(d^{2}{\vec{w}}.{\vec{v}}).{\vec{v}}+d{\vec{w}}.d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{v}}-2d{\vec{v}}.d{\vec{w}}.{\vec{v}}-(d^{2}{\vec{v}}.{\vec{v}}).{\vec{w}}+d{\vec{v}}.d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}.}

(9.39)

Answer
.

\displaystyle{{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}})=}

\displaystyle{{}{D({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}})\over Dt}-d{\vec{v}}.({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}})={D({D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}-d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}})\over Dt}-d{\vec{v}}.({D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}-d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}})}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}{D^{2}{\vec{w}}\over Dt^{2}}-{D(d{\vec{v}})\over Dt}.{\vec{w}}-d{\vec{v}}.{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}-d{\vec{v}}.{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}+d{\vec{v}}.d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}},}

thus (
9.39
)
1
, thus (
9.39
)
2
.

9.5
Lie derivative of a differential form

When the Lie derivative of a vector field
{\vec{w}}
cannot be obtained by direct measurements, you need to use a “measuring device” (Germain: To know the weight of a suitcase you have to lift it: You use work).

Here we consider a measuring device which is a differential form
\alpha
. So, if
{\vec{w}}
is a vector field
then
f=\alpha.{\vec{v}}
is a scalar function, and (
9.13
) gives

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}(\alpha.{\vec{w}})={D(\alpha.{\vec{w}})\over Dt}={D\alpha\over Dt}.{\vec{w}}+\alpha.{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}
, thus

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}(\alpha.{\vec{w}})=\underbrace{{D\alpha\over Dt}.{\vec{w}}+\alpha.d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}}_{\rightarrow({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\alpha).{\vec{w}}}+\underbrace{\alpha.{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}-\alpha.d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}}_{=\alpha.{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}}:

(9.40)

Definition 9.12

Let
\alpha
be a differential form.
The Lie derivative of
\alpha
along
{\vec{v}}
is
the differential form

\framebox{$\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\alpha:={D\alpha\over Dt}+\alpha.d{\vec{v}}$}={\partial\alpha\over\partial t}+d\alpha.{\vec{v}}+\alpha.d{\vec{v}}.

(9.41)

(An equivalent definition is given at (
9.47
).) I.e., for all vector field
{\vec{w}}
,

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\alpha.{\vec{w}}:={D\alpha\over Dt}.{\vec{w}}+\alpha.d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}\quad(={\partial\alpha\over\partial t}.{\vec{w}}+(d\alpha.{\vec{v}}).{\vec{w}}+\alpha.d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}).

(9.42)

The definition of
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\alpha
, cf. (
9.41
), immediately gives the “derivation property”

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}(\alpha.{\vec{w}})=({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\alpha).{\vec{w}}+\alpha.({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}})\quad(\hbox{i{.}e{.}\ ${\cal L}_{\vec{v}}$ is a derivation}).

(9.43)

Quantification:
Relative to a basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
and with
[\cdot]:=[\cdot]_{|{\vec{e}}}
,

\framebox{$\displaystyle[{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\alpha]=[{D\alpha\over Dt}]+[\alpha].[d{\vec{v}}]$}\quad\hbox{(row matrix) }=[{\partial\alpha\over\partial t}]+[d\alpha.{\vec{v}}]+[\alpha].[d{\vec{v}}].

(9.44)

Thus

[{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\alpha.{\vec{w}}]=[{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\alpha].[{\vec{w}}]=[{\partial\alpha\over\partial t}].[{\vec{w}}]+[d\alpha.{\vec{v}}].[{\vec{w}}]+[\alpha].[d{\vec{v}}].[{\vec{w}}].

(9.45)

Exercice 9.13

Prove (
9.44
) with components. And prove
[d\alpha.{\vec{v}}]=[{\vec{v}}]^{T}.[d\alpha]^{T}
(row matrix),
thus
[d\alpha.{\vec{v}}].[{\vec{w}}]=[{\vec{v}}]^{T}.[d\alpha]^{T}.[{\vec{w}}]=[{\vec{w}}]^{T}.[d\alpha].[{\vec{v}}]
.

Answer
. Basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
, dual basis
(\pi_{ei})
, thus (
9.41
) gives

[{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\alpha]=[{D\alpha\over Dt}]+[\alpha.d{\vec{v}}]
.
Let
\alpha=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\pi_{ei}
,
{\vec{v}}=\sum_{i}v_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,
d{\vec{v}}=\sum_{ij}v_{i|j}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes\pi_{ej}

(tensorial writing convenient for calculations), i.e.
[d{\vec{v}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[v_{i|j}]
, thus

\alpha.d{\vec{v}}=\sum_{ij}\alpha_{i}v_{i|j}\pi_{ej}
,
thus
[\alpha.d{\vec{v}}]_{|\pi_{e}}=[\alpha]_{|\pi_{e}}.[d{\vec{v}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}
(row matrix).
And
d\alpha=\sum_{ij}\alpha_{i|j}\pi_{ei}\otimes\pi_{ej}
, i.e.
[d\alpha]_{|\pi_{e}}=[\alpha_{i|j}]
, gives

d\alpha.{\vec{v}}=\sum_{ij}\alpha_{i|j}v_{j}\pi_{ei}=\sum_{ij}v_{i}\alpha_{j|i}\pi_{ej}
,
and
[d\alpha.{\vec{v}}]_{|\pi_{e}}
is a row matrix (
d\alpha.{\vec{v}}
is a differential form), thus

[d\alpha.{\vec{v}}]_{|\pi_{e}}=[{\vec{v}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}.[d\alpha]_{|\pi_{e}}^{T}
.
(Or compute
(d\alpha.{\vec{v}}).{\vec{w}}=\sum_{ij}\alpha_{i|j}v_{j}w_{i}=[{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}.[d\alpha]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{v}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[{\vec{v}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}.[d\alpha]_{|\pi_{e}}^{T}.[{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}
.)

Exercice 9.14

Let
\alpha
be a differential form, and let
\alpha_{t}(p):=\alpha(t,p)
. Prove, when
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
is a diffeomorphism,

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\alpha=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\forall t\in[{t_{0}},T],\;\alpha_{t}=({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})_{*}\alpha_{t_{0}}.

(9.46)

I.e.:
{D\alpha\over Dt}=-\alpha.d{\vec{v}}

\Longleftrightarrow

\alpha_{t}({p_{t}})=\alpha_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}}).{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})^{-1}
for all
t
, when
{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})
.

Answer
.
\Leftarrow
:
If
\alpha_{t}(p(t))=\alpha_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}}).{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})^{-1}
,
then
\alpha(t,p(t)).{F^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})=\alpha_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}})
,
thus
{D\alpha\over Dt}(t,{p_{t}}).{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})+\alpha_{t}({p_{t}}).{\partial{F^{t_{0}}}\over\partial t}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})=0
,
thus
{D\alpha\over Dt}(t,p(t)).{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})+\alpha_{t}({p_{t}}).d{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}}).{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})=0
,
thus
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\alpha=0
, since
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
is a diffeomorphism.

\Rightarrow
:
If
\beta(t):=({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})_{*}\alpha_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}})=\alpha_{t}(p(t)).{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})
(pull-back at
({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})
),
then
\beta(t)=\alpha(t,p(t)).{F^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})
,
thus
\beta^{\prime}(t)={D\alpha\over Dt}(t,{p_{t}}).{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})+\alpha(t,{p_{t}}).d{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}}).{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})=0
(hypothesis
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\alpha=0
), thus
\beta(t)=\beta({t_{0}})=\alpha_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}})
.

Remark 9.15

A definition equivalent to (
9.41
) is, cf. (
9.10
),

\displaystyle{{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\alpha(t,{p_{t}})}

\displaystyle{{}:=\lim_{\tau\rightarrow t}{({\Phi^{t}_{\tau}})^{*}\alpha_{\tau}({p_{t}})-\alpha_{t}({p_{t}})\over\tau-t}\quad(=\lim_{\tau\rightarrow t}{\alpha_{\tau}({p_{\tau}}).d{\Phi^{t}_{\tau}}({p_{t}})-\alpha_{t}({p_{t}})\over\tau-t})}

\displaystyle{{}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{D({\Phi^{t*}_{\tau}}\alpha_{\tau}({p_{t}}))\over D\tau}_{|\tau=t}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{D(\alpha_{\tau}^{*}({p_{t}}))\over D\tau}_{|\tau=t}\quad(={D(\alpha_{\tau}({p_{\tau}}).d{\Phi^{t}_{\tau}}({p_{t}}))\over D\tau}_{|\tau=t}).}

(9.47)

Indeed, if
\beta(\tau)=({\Phi^{t}_{\tau}})^{*}\alpha_{\tau}({p_{t}})=\alpha_{\tau}({p_{\tau}}).d{\Phi^{t}_{\tau}}({p_{t}})
,
then
\beta^{\prime}(\tau)
and then
\tau=t
give (
9.41
).

Exercice 9.16

{\vec{v}}
and
\alpha
being
C^{2}
, prove:

\displaystyle{{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\alpha)=}

\displaystyle{{}{\partial^{2}\alpha\over\partial t^{2}}+2d{\partial\alpha\over\partial t}.{\vec{v}}+2{\partial\alpha\over\partial t}.d{\vec{v}}+d\alpha.{\partial{\vec{v}}\over\partial t}+\alpha.{\partial d{\vec{v}}\over\partial t}}

\displaystyle{{}+(d^{2}\alpha.{\vec{v}}).{\vec{v}}+d\alpha.(d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{v}})+2(d\alpha.{\vec{v}}).d{\vec{v}}+\alpha.(d^{2}{\vec{v}}.{\vec{v}})+(\alpha.d{\vec{v}}).d{\vec{v}}.}

(9.48)

Answer
. (
9.41
) gives

\displaystyle{{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\alpha)=}

\displaystyle{{}{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}({\partial\alpha\over\partial t})+{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}(d\alpha.{\vec{v}})+{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}(\alpha.d{\vec{v}})}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}{\partial^{2}\alpha\over\partial t^{2}}+d{\partial\alpha\over\partial t}.{\vec{v}}+{\partial\alpha\over\partial t}.d{\vec{v}}+{\partial(d\alpha.{\vec{v}})\over\partial t}+d(d\alpha.{\vec{v}}).{\vec{v}}+(d\alpha.{\vec{v}}).d{\vec{v}}+{\partial(\alpha.d{\vec{v}})\over\partial t}+d(\alpha.d{\vec{v}}).{\vec{v}}+(\alpha.d{\vec{v}}).d{\vec{v}}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}{\partial^{2}\alpha\over\partial t^{2}}+d{\partial\alpha\over\partial t}.{\vec{v}}+{\partial\alpha\over\partial t}.d{\vec{v}}+{\partial d\alpha\over\partial t}.{\vec{v}}+d\alpha.{\partial{\vec{v}}\over\partial t}+(d^{2}\alpha.{\vec{v}}).{\vec{v}}+d\alpha.(d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{v}})+(d\alpha.{\vec{v}}).d{\vec{v}}}

\displaystyle{{}+{\partial\alpha\over\partial t}.d{\vec{v}}+\alpha.{\partial d{\vec{v}}\over\partial t}+(d\alpha.{\vec{v}}).d{\vec{v}}+\alpha.d^{2}{\vec{v}}.{\vec{v}}+(\alpha.d{\vec{v}}).d{\vec{v}}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}{\partial^{2}\alpha\over\partial t^{2}}+2d{\partial\alpha\over\partial t}.{\vec{v}}+2{\partial\alpha\over\partial t}.d{\vec{v}}+d\alpha.{\partial{\vec{v}}\over\partial t}+(d^{2}\alpha.{\vec{v}}).{\vec{v}}+d\alpha.(d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{v}})+2(d\alpha.{\vec{v}}).d{\vec{v}}+\alpha.{\partial d{\vec{v}}\over\partial t}}

\displaystyle{{}+\alpha.(d^{2}{\vec{v}}.{\vec{v}})+(\alpha.d{\vec{v}}).d{\vec{v}}.}

9.6
Incompatibility with Riesz representation vectors

The Lie derivative has nothing to do with any inner dot product
(the Lie derivative does not compare two vectors, contrary to a Cauchy type approach).

Here we introduce a Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
and show that the Lie derivative of a linear form
\alpha
is not trivially deduced from the Lie derivative of a Riesz representation vector of
\alpha
(which one?).
(Same issue as at §
7.2
.)

Let
\alpha
be a Eulerian differential form; Then let
\vec{a}_{g}(t,p)\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
be the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Riesz representation vector of the linear form
\alpha(t,p)\in{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{*}}
: So, for all Eulerian vector field
{\vec{w}}
,

\alpha.{\vec{w}}=(\vec{a}_{g},{\vec{w}})_{g}\qquad(=\vec{a}_{g}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,{\vec{w}}),

(9.49)

which means
\alpha(t,p).{\vec{w}}(t,p)=(\vec{a}_{g}(t,p),{\vec{w}}(t,p))_{g}
at all admissible
