(t,p)
.
This defines the Eulerian vector field
\vec{a}_{g}
(not intrinsic to
\alpha
:
\vec{a}_{g}
depends on the choice of
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
, cf. (
F.12
)).

Proposition 9.17

For all
{\vec{v}},{\vec{w}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
,

{\partial\alpha\over\partial t}.{\vec{w}}=({\partial\vec{a}_{g}\over\partial t},{\vec{w}})_{g},\quad(d\alpha.{\vec{v}}).{\vec{w}}=(d\vec{a}_{g}.{\vec{v}},{\vec{w}})_{g},\quad{D\alpha\over Dt}.{\vec{w}}=({D\vec{a}_{g}\over Dt},{\vec{w}})_{g}.

(9.50)

Thus

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\alpha.{\vec{w}}=({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\vec{a}_{g},{\vec{w}})_{g}+(\vec{a}_{g},(d{\vec{v}}{+}d{\vec{v}}^{T}).{\vec{w}})_{g},\quad\hbox{and}\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\alpha.{\vec{w}}\neq({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\vec{a}_{g},{\vec{w}})_{g}$}\quad\hbox{in general}.

(9.51)

So
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\vec{a}_{g}
is
not
the Riesz representation vector of
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\alpha
(but for solid body motions).
(Expected: A Lie derivative is covariant objective, see §
11.4
, and the use of an inner dot product ruins this objectivity.)

Proof.
A Euclidean dot product
g(\cdot,\cdot)
is bilinear constant and uniform, thus:

\alpha.{\vec{w}}=(\vec{a}_{g},{\vec{w}})_{g}
gives

{\partial\alpha\over\partial t}.{\vec{w}}+\alpha.{\partial{\vec{w}}\over\partial t}=({\partial\vec{a}_{g}\over\partial t},{\vec{w}})_{g}+(\vec{a}_{g},{\partial{\vec{w}}\over\partial t})_{g}
, with

\alpha.{\partial{\vec{w}}\over\partial t}=(\vec{a}_{g},{\partial{\vec{w}}\over\partial t})_{g}
, thus we are left with

{\partial\alpha\over\partial t}.{\vec{w}}=({\partial\vec{a}_{g}\over\partial t},{\vec{w}})_{g}
, for all
{\vec{w}}
.

\alpha.{\vec{w}}=(\vec{a}_{g},{\vec{w}})_{g}
gives

d(\alpha.{\vec{w}}).{\vec{v}}=d(\vec{a}_{g},{\vec{w}})_{g}.{\vec{v}}
for all
{\vec{v}},{\vec{w}}
,
thus

(d\alpha.{\vec{v}}).{\vec{w}}+\alpha.(d{\vec{w}}.{\vec{v}})=(d\vec{a}_{g}.{\vec{v}},{\vec{w}})_{g}+(\vec{a}_{g},d{\vec{w}}.{\vec{v}})_{g}
, with

\alpha.(d{\vec{w}}.{\vec{v}})=(\vec{a}_{g},d{\vec{w}}.{\vec{v}})_{g}
, thus we are left with

(d\alpha.{\vec{v}}).{\vec{w}}=(d\vec{a}_{g}.{\vec{v}},{\vec{w}})_{g}
.

Thus
{D\alpha\over Dt}.{\vec{w}}=({D\vec{a}_{g}\over Dt},{\vec{w}})_{g}
.

Thus

({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\alpha).{\vec{w}}={D\alpha\over Dt}.{\vec{w}}+\alpha.d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}=({D\vec{a}_{g}\over Dt},{\vec{w}})_{g}+(\vec{a}_{g},d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}})_{g}=({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\vec{a}_{g}+d{\vec{v}}.\vec{a}_{g},{\vec{w}})_{g}+(d{\vec{v}}^{T}_{g}.\vec{a}_{g},{\vec{w}})_{g}
.

Remark 9.18

Chorus: a “differential form” (measuring instrument, covariant) should not be confused with a “vector field” (object to be measured, contravariant); Thus, the use of a dot product (which one?) and the Riesz representation theorem should be restricted for computational purposes, after an objective equation has been established.
See also remark
F.13
.

9.7
Lie derivative of a tensor

The Lie derivative of any tensor of order
\geq 2
is defined thanks to

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}(T\otimes S)=({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}T)\otimes S+T\otimes({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}S)\quad(\hbox{derivation formula}).

(9.52)

(Or direct definition:
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}T({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})={D(({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{*}T_{t})({p_{t_{0}}})\over Dt}_{|t={t_{0}}}
).

9.7.1
Lie derivative of a mixed tensor

Let
T_{m}\in{T^{1}_{1}(\Omega)}
, and
T_{m}
is called a mixed tensor; Its Lie derivative, called the Jaumann derivative, is given by

\framebox{$\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}T_{m}={DT_{m}\over Dt}-d{\vec{v}}.T_{m}+T_{m}.d{\vec{v}}$}={\partial T_{m}\over\partial t}+dT_{m}.{\vec{v}}-d{\vec{v}}.T_{m}+T_{m}.d{\vec{v}}.

(9.53)

Can be checked with an elementary tensor
T={\vec{w}}\otimes\alpha
:
we have
d({\vec{w}}\otimes\alpha).{\vec{v}}=(d{\vec{w}}.{\vec{v}})\otimes\alpha+{\vec{w}}\otimes(d\alpha.{\vec{v}})

and
(d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}})\otimes\alpha=d{\vec{v}}.({\vec{w}}\otimes\alpha)
, and

{\vec{w}}\otimes(\alpha.d{\vec{v}})=({\vec{w}}\otimes\alpha).d{\vec{v}}

, thus
(
9.52
) gives

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}({\vec{w}}\otimes\alpha)=({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}})\otimes\alpha+{\vec{w}}\otimes({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\alpha)

={\partial{\vec{w}}\over\partial t}\otimes\alpha+(d{\vec{w}}.{\vec{v}})\otimes\alpha-(d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}})\otimes\alpha+{\vec{w}}\otimes{\partial\alpha\over\partial t}+{\vec{w}}\otimes(d\alpha.{\vec{v}})+{\vec{w}}\otimes(\alpha.d{\vec{v}})

={\partial{\vec{w}}\otimes\alpha\over\partial t}+d({\vec{w}}\otimes\alpha).{\vec{v}}-d{\vec{v}}.({\vec{w}}\otimes\alpha)+({\vec{w}}\otimes\alpha).d{\vec{v}}
.

Quantification.
Relative to a basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
:

[{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}T_{m}]=[{DT_{m}\over Dt}]-[d{\vec{v}}].[T_{m}]+[T_{m}].[d{\vec{v}}]

(9.54)

(the signs
\mp
are mixed).
“Mixed” also refers to positions of indices (up and down with duality notations):

T_{m}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}T^{i}{}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
with the dual basis
(e^{i})
, i.e.
[T_{m}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[T^{i}{}_{j}]
.

Exercice 9.19

With components, prove (
9.54
).

Answer
.
{\partial T_{m}\over\partial t}=\sum_{ij}{\partial T^{i}{}_{j}\over\partial t}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
,

dT_{m}=\sum_{ijk}T^{i}{}_{j|k}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}\otimes e^{k}
,

{\vec{v}}=\sum_{i}v^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,
d{\vec{v}}=\sum_{ij}v^{i}_{|j}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
, thus

dT_{m}.{\vec{v}}=\sum_{ijk}T^{i}{}_{j|k}v^{k}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
,

d{\vec{v}}.T_{m}=\sum_{ijk}v^{i}_{|k}T^{k}{}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
,

T_{m}.d{\vec{v}}=\sum_{ijk}T^{i}{}_{k}v^{k}_{|j}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
.

9.7.2
Lie derivative of a up-tensor

Recall: If
L\in{\cal L}(E;F)
(a linear map) then its adjoint
L^{*}\in{\cal L}(F^{*};E^{*})
is defined by, cf. §
A.12
,

\forall m\in F^{*},\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle L^{*}.m:=m.L$},\quad\hbox{i{.}e{.},}\quad\forall m,{\vec{u}}\in(F^{*}\times E),\quad(L^{*}.m).{\vec{u}}=m.L.{\vec{u}}.

(9.55)

(There is no inner dot product involved here.)
In particular,
d{\vec{v}}^{*}.m:=m.d{\vec{v}}
for all
m\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n*}_{t}}
, i.e.
(d{\vec{v}}^{*}.m).{\vec{u}}=(m.d{\vec{v}}).{\vec{u}}=m.(d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{u}})
for all
m\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n*}_{t}}
and all
{\vec{u}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
.

Let
T_{u}\in{T^{2}_{0}(\Omega)}
, and
T_{u}
is called a up tensor; Its Lie derivative is called the upper-convected (Maxwell) derivative or the Oldroyd derivative and is given by

\framebox{$\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}T_{u}={DT_{u}\over Dt}-d{\vec{v}}.T_{u}-T_{u}.d{\vec{v}}^{*}$}={\partial T_{u}\over\partial t}+dT_{u}.{\vec{v}}-d{\vec{v}}.T_{u}-T_{u}.d{\vec{v}}^{*}.

(9.56)

Can be checked with an elementary tensor
T={\vec{u}}\otimes{\vec{w}}
and

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}({\vec{u}}\otimes{\vec{w}})=({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{u}})\otimes{\vec{w}}+{\vec{u}}\otimes({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}})
.

Quantification.
Relative to a basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
:

[{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}T_{u}]=[{DT_{u}\over Dt}]-[d{\vec{v}}].[T_{u}]-[T_{u}].[d{\vec{v}}]^{T}.

(9.57)

“up” also refers to positions of indices (with duality notations):

T_{u}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}T^{ij}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes{\vec{e}}_{j}
with the dual basis
(e^{i})
, i.e.
[T_{u}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[T^{ij}]
.

Exercice 9.20

With components, prove (
9.56
).

Answer
.
{\partial T_{u}\over\partial t}=\sum_{ij}{\partial T^{ij}\over\partial t}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes{\vec{e}}_{j}
,

dT_{u}=\sum_{ijk}T^{ij}_{|k}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes{\vec{e}}_{j}\otimes e^{k}
,

{\vec{v}}=\sum_{i}v^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,
d{\vec{v}}=\sum_{ij}v^{i}_{|j}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
,

d{\vec{v}}^{*}=\sum_{ij}v^{j}_{|i}e^{i}\otimes{\vec{e}}_{j}
,
thus

dT_{u}.{\vec{v}}=\sum_{ijk}T^{ij}_{|k}v^{k}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
,

d{\vec{v}}.T_{u}=\sum_{ijk}v^{i}_{|k}T^{kj}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes{\vec{e}}_{j}
,

T_{u}.d{\vec{v}}^{*}=\sum_{ijk}T^{ik}v^{j}_{|k}e^{i}\otimes{\vec{e}}_{j}
.

9.7.3
Lie derivative of a down-tensor

Let
T_{d}\in{T^{0}_{2}(\Omega)}
, and
T_{d}
is called a down tensor; The Lie derivative is called the lower-convected Maxwell derivative and is given by

\framebox{$\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}T_{d}={DT_{d}\over Dt}+T_{d}.d{\vec{v}}+d{\vec{v}}^{*}.T_{d}$}={\partial T_{d}\over\partial t}+dT_{d}.{\vec{v}}+T_{d}.d{\vec{v}}+d{\vec{v}}^{*}.T_{d}.

(9.58)

Can be checked with an elementary tensor
T=\ell\otimes m
and

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}(\ell\otimes m)=({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\ell)\otimes m+\ell\otimes({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}m)
.

Quantification.
Relative to a basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
:

[{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}T_{d}]=[{DT_{d}\over Dt}]+[T_{d}].[d{\vec{v}}]+[d{\vec{v}}]^{T}.[T_{d}].

(9.59)

“down” also refers to positions of indices (with duality notations):

T_{d}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}T_{ij}e^{i}\otimes e^{j}
with the dual basis
(e^{i})
, i.e.
[T_{d}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[T_{ij}]
.

Exercice 9.21

With components, prove (
9.59
).

Answer
.
{\partial T_{d}\over\partial t}=\sum_{ij}{\partial T_{ij}\over\partial t}e^{i}\otimes e^{j}
,

dT_{d}=\sum_{ijk}T_{ij|k}e^{i}\otimes e^{j}\otimes e^{k}
,

{\vec{v}}=\sum_{i}v^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,
d{\vec{v}}=\sum_{ij}v^{i}_{|j}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
,

d{\vec{v}}^{*}=\sum_{ij}v^{j}_{|i}e^{i}\otimes{\vec{e}}_{j}
,
thus

dT_{d}.{\vec{v}}=\sum_{ijk}T_{ij|k}v^{k}e^{i}\otimes e^{j}
,

T_{d}.d{\vec{v}}=\sum_{ijk}T_{ik}v^{k}_{|j}e^{i}\otimes{\vec{e}}_{j}
,

d{\vec{v}}^{*}.T_{d}=\sum_{ijk}v^{k}_{|i}T_{kj}e^{i}\otimes{\vec{e}}_{j}
.

Example 9.22

Let
g=(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}\in{T^{0}_{2}(\Omega)}
be a constant and uniform metric (a unique inner dot product for all
t,p
, e.g., a Euclidean dot product at all
t
). Then
{Dg\over Dt}=0
, thus
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}g=0+g.d{\vec{v}}+d{\vec{v}}^{*}.g
,
thus
[{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}g]=[g].[d{\vec{v}}]+[d{\vec{v}}]^{T}.[g]
.

Part IV
Velocity-addition formula

10
Change of referential and velocity-addition formula

10.0
Issue and result (summary)

The velocity-addition formula is (in classical mechanics)

{{\vec{v}}_{\!A}}={{\vec{v}}_{\!B}}+{\vec{v}_{\!D}},

(10.1)

where

{{\vec{v}}_{\!A}}
,
{{\vec{v}}_{\!B}}
and
{\vec{v}_{\!D}}
are the absolute, relative and drive velocity,

{{\vec{v}}_{\!A}}
and
{\vec{v}_{\!D}}
being velocities described by an observer A with his referential
{{\cal R}_{\!A}}=({O_{\!A}},({\vec{A}}_{i}))
and
{{\vec{v}}_{\!B}}
being a velocity described by an observer B with his referential
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}=({O_{\!B}},({\vec{B}}_{i}))
.
But (
10.1
) is problematic (inconsistent):

\bullet
The velocities
{{\vec{v}}_{\!A}}
and
{\vec{v}_{\!D}}
are quantified in
{{\cal R}_{\!A}}
, e.g. expressed in foot/s by the absolute observer,

\bullet
The velocity
{{\vec{v}}_{\!B}}
is a quantified in
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}
, e.g. expressed in metre/s by the relative observer,

Thus (
10.1
) with
{{\vec{v}}_{\!B}}+{\vec{v}_{\!D}}
tells that you add metre/s and foot/s… absurd. So:

Question: What are we missing (and what does (
10.1
) really mean)?

Answer: We miss a functional link: The translator between A and B. Summary:

Call
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
the motion of a observed object
{\it O\!b\!j}
;

{\widetilde{\Phi}}
is
quantified
by A in his referential
{{\cal R}_{\!A}}=({O_{\!A}},({\vec{A}}_{i}))
as the “motion”
{\vec{\varphi}_{\!A}}=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{\widetilde{\Phi}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}
, and
is
quantified
by B in his referential
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}=({O_{\!B}},({\vec{B}}_{i}))
as the “motion”
{\vec{\varphi}_{\!B}}=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}{\widetilde{\Phi}}}]_{|{\vec{B}}}
.
At
t
, the translator
\Theta
connects these numerical values:
{\vec{\varphi}_{\!A}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})=\Theta(t,{\vec{\varphi}_{\!B}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}))
.
Thus

{\partial{\vec{\varphi}_{\!A}}\over\partial t}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})={\partial\Theta\over\partial t}(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt})+d\Theta(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{\partial{\vec{\varphi}_{\!B}}\over\partial t}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
, i.e.
{{\vec{v}}_{\!A}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!At})={\partial\Theta\over\partial t}(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt})+d\Theta(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{{\vec{v}}_{\!B}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt})

where

\vec{x}_{\!At}={\vec{\varphi}_{\!A}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
and
\vec{x}_{\!Bt}={\vec{\varphi}_{\!B}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
.
Then call

d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})={{\vec{v}}_{Bt*}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})=\hbox{``the translated relative velocity at\penalty\ $t$ from B to\penalty\ A''},

(10.2)

thus, with
{\partial\Theta\over\partial t}(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt})={\vec{v}_{\!D}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!At})
the drive velocity,
which gives
{{\vec{v}}_{\!A}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!At})=\vec{v}_{\!B*}(t,\vec{x}_{\!At})+{\vec{v}_{\!D}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!At})
:
so

{{\vec{v}}_{\!A}}=\vec{v}_{\!B*}+{\vec{v}_{\!D}}\quad=\quad\hbox{the velocity addition formula in ${{\cal R}_{\!A}}$},

(10.3)

\hbox{i{.}e{.}: (Absolute velocity) = (Translated relative velocity) + (Drive velocity)}.

In other words, with
{\vec{v}}
the velocity of
{\it O\!b\!j}
and with
{{\vec{v}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}
the velocity of
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}
in
{{\cal R}_{\!A}}
: For all
{p_{t}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
,

[{\vec{v}}_{t}({p_{t}})]_{|{\vec{A}}}=d{\Theta_{t}}.[{\vec{v}}_{t}({p_{t}})]_{{\vec{B}}}+[{{\vec{v}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!Bt}}}({p_{t}})]_{|{\vec{A}}},

(10.4)

relation between the numerical values of the velocities stored by A and B.

Example 10.1

Translation motion of
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}
in
{{\cal R}_{\!A}}
, so
[{{\vec{v}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!Bt}}}({p_{t}})]_{|{\vec{A}}}=[{{\vec{v}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!Bt}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}
is independent of
{p_{t}}
;
And, e.g. with
({\vec{B}_{it}})=\lambda({\vec{A}_{it}})
(e.g.
{\vec{A}}_{i}
in foot and
{\vec{B}}_{i}
in meter give
\lambda\simeq 3.28
),

d{\Theta_{t}}=\lambda I
, hence
[{\vec{v}}_{t}({p_{t}})]_{|{\vec{A}}}=\lambda[{\vec{v}}_{t}({p_{t}})]_{{\vec{B}}}+[{{\vec{v}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!Bt}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}
,
which is the expected relation (“sum of the velocities with the good units”).

Example 10.2

Motion of the Earth around the Sun: See §
10.11
.

10.1
Referentials and “matrix motions”

10.1.1
Absolute and relative referentials

Classical mechanics framework: Time and space are decoupled, all the observers share the same time unit (e.g. the second)
and live in “our” Universe modeled as
{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
(affine space) with its usual associated vector space
{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}
. In the following, the affine space is
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
associated to the vector space
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
,
n\in\{1,2,3\}
.

An observer A, which we will call the absolute observer, chooses a (rigid body) object
{\it O\!b\!j}\!{\cal R}_{\!A}
in the Universe,
chooses one particle in
{\it O\!b\!j}\!{\cal R}_{\!A}
, calls
{O_{\!At}}
its position at
t
,
and chooses three more particles in
{\it O\!b\!j}\!{\cal R}_{\!A}
, calls
{P_{\!Ati}}
their positions at
t
(in the Universe),
such that the bi-point vectors
{\vec{A}_{it}}:=\overrightarrow{{O_{\!At}}{P_{\!Ati}}}
make a basis in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
.
He has thus built his (Cartesian) referential
{{\cal R}_{\!At}}=({O_{\!At}},({\vec{A}_{it}}))
, called the absolute referential,
and written
{{\cal R}_{\!A}}=({O_{\!A}},({\vec{A}}_{i}))
when used by A. E.g.
{\it O\!b\!j}\!{\cal R}_{\!A}
is the “Sun extended to infinity”, and at
t
,
{O_{\!At}}
is the position of the center of the Sun in the Universe,
({\vec{A}}_{it})
is a Euclidean basis in foot fixed relative to stars.

An observer B, which we will call the relative observer, proceeds similarly: He chooses a (rigid body) object
{\it O\!b\!j}\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}
in the Universe,
builds his Cartesian referential
{{\cal R}_{\!Bt}}=({O_{\!Bt}},({\vec{B}_{it}}))
, called the relative referential,
written
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}=({O_{\!B}},({\vec{B}}_{i}))
when used by B.
E.g.
{\it O\!b\!j}\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}
is the “Earth extended to infinity”, and at
t
,
{O_{\!Bt}}
is the position of the center of the Earth and
({\vec{B}}_{it})
is a Euclidean basis in metre fixed relative to the Earth.

{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}
is the vectorial space of
n*1
matrices (column matrices).
A and B call
{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{A}\!)
and
{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{B}\!)
the affine spaces of
n*1
matrices made of the “matrix positions”

[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!At}}{p_{t}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}
and
[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!Bt}}{p_{t}}}]_{|{\vec{B}}}
where
{p_{t}}
is the position at
t
of a particle in the Universe.

If a function
\varphi
is given as
\varphi(t,x)
, then
\varphi_{t}(x):=\varphi(t,x)
, and conversely.

10.1.2
Motion of a material object
{\it O\!b\!j}

An object
{\it O\!b\!j}
is considered by all observers. Its motion in the Universe is

{\widetilde{\Phi}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr[t_{1},t_{2}]\times{\it O\!b\!j}&\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\cr(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})&\rightarrow{p_{t}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})=\hbox{position of the particle\penalty\ ${P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}$ at t in the Universe}.\crcr}}\,\right.

(10.5)

At
t
at
{p_{t}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
, the Eulerian velocities and accelerations of
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
are

{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}})={\partial{\widetilde{\Phi}}\over\partial t}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})\quad\hbox{and}\quad{\vec{\gamma}}(t,{p_{t}})={\partial^{2}{\widetilde{\Phi}}\over\partial^{2}t}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})\quad(\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}).

(10.6)

10.1.3
Quantification: Absolute and relative “motion” of
{\it O\!b\!j}

At
t
, the position
{p_{t}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
of a particle
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}\in{\it O\!b\!j}

is spotted by A, resp. B, with the bi-point vectors
\overrightarrow{{O_{\!At}}{p_{t}}}
, resp.
\overrightarrow{{O_{\!Bt}}{p_{t}}}
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
,
which components is stored by A, resp. B, in his referentials: With

\overrightarrow{{O_{\!At}}{p_{t}}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x_{\!Ati}{\vec{A}_{it}}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\overrightarrow{{O_{\!Bt}}{p_{t}}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x_{\!Bti}{\vec{B}_{it}},

(10.7)

and with
({\vec{E}}_{i})
the canonical basis in
{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}
, the
n*1
matrices

\vec{x}_{\!At}:=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{p_{t}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}=\pmatrix{x_{\!At1}\cr\vdots\cr x_{\!Atn}\cr}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x_{\!Ati}{\vec{E}}_{i},\quad\hbox{and}\quad\vec{x}_{\!Bt}:=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}{p_{t}}}]_{|{\vec{B}}}=\pmatrix{x_{\!Bt1}\cr\vdots\cr x_{\!Btn}\cr}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x_{\!Bti}{\vec{E}}_{i},

(10.8)

are stored by A and B.
(Initial notation:
\vec{x}_{\!At}:=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!At}}{p_{t}}}]_{|({\vec{A}_{it}})}
, but here
{O_{\!At}}
and
({\vec{A}_{it}})
are fixed in
{{\cal R}_{\!A}}
, idem for B.)

Mind the notations:
{p_{t}}
is a point,
\overrightarrow{{O_{\!At}}{p_{t}}}
is a vector,
\vec{x}_{\!At}
is a column matrix (components).

This defines the “absolute motion”
{\vec{\varphi}_{\!A}}
and “relative motion”
{\vec{\varphi}_{\!B}}
of
{\it O\!b\!j}
(matrix valued):

{\vec{\varphi}_{\!A}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr[t_{1},t_{2}]{\times}{\it O\!b\!j}&\rightarrow{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{A}\!)\cr(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})&\rightarrow\framebox{$\displaystyle{\vec{\varphi}_{\!A}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}):=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})}]_{|{\vec{A}}}$}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x_{\!Ai}(t){\vec{E}}_{i}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\vec{x}_{\!A}(t)=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}p(t)}]_{|{\vec{A}}},\crcr}}\,\right.

(10.9)

{\vec{\varphi}_{\!B}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr[t_{1},t_{2}]{\times}{\it O\!b\!j}&\rightarrow{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{B}\!)\cr(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})&\rightarrow\framebox{$\displaystyle{\vec{\varphi}_{\!B}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}):=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}{\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})}]_{|{\vec{B}}}$}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x_{\!Bi}(t){\vec{E}}_{i}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\vec{x}_{\!B}(t)=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}p(t)}]_{|{\vec{B}}}.\crcr}}\,\right.

(10.10)

And the “absolute” and “relative” velocities and accelerations of
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
are (matrix valued in
{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}
):

\framebox{$\displaystyle{{\vec{v}}_{\!A}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!At}):=[{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}})]_{|{\vec{A}}}$}\quad\hbox{and}\quad{{\vec{\gamma}}_{A}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!At}):=[{\vec{\gamma}}(t,{p_{t}})]_{|{\vec{A}}},\quad\hbox{when}\quad\vec{x}_{\!At}:=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{p_{t}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}},

(10.11)

\framebox{$\displaystyle{{\vec{v}}_{\!B}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt}):=[{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}})]_{|{\vec{B}}}$}\quad\hbox{and}\quad{{\vec{\gamma}}_{B}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt}):=[{\vec{\gamma}}(t,{p_{t}})]_{|{\vec{B}}},\quad\hbox{when}\quad\vec{x}_{\!Bt}:=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}{p_{t}}}]_{|{\vec{B}}}.

(10.12)

Exercice 10.3

Prove:
{{\vec{v}}_{\!A}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!At})={\partial{\vec{\varphi}_{\!A}}\over\partial t}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
.

Answer
.
\overrightarrow{{O_{\!At}}{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}(t)}=\sum_{i}x_{\!Ai}(t){\vec{A}}_{i}(t)
gives

{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}})=\sum_{i}x_{\!Ai}{}^{\prime}(t){\vec{A}}_{i}(t)+x_{\!Ai}(t){\vec{A}}_{i}{}^{\prime}(t)
,
thus

[{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}})]_{|{\vec{A}}}=\sum_{i}x_{\!Ai}{}^{\prime}(t)[{\vec{A}}_{i}(t)]_{|{\vec{A}}}+x_{\!Ai}(t)[{\vec{A}}_{i}{}^{\prime}(t)]_{|{\vec{A}}}

(since
{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}
is a vector space)
=\sum_{i}x_{\!Ai}{}^{\prime}(t){\vec{E}}_{i}+[\vec{0}]
(the
{\vec{A}}_{i}(t)
are static in
{{\cal R}_{\!A}}
:
[{\vec{A}}_{i}{}^{\prime}(t)]_{|{\vec{A}}}=[\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{{\vec{A}}_{i}(t{+}h)-{\vec{A}}_{i}(t)\over h}]_{|{\vec{A}}}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{[{\vec{A}}_{i}(t{+}h)]_{|{\vec{A}}}-[{\vec{A}}_{i}(t)]_{|{\vec{A}}}\over h}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{{\vec{E}}_{i}-{\vec{E}}_{i}\over h}=0
).
And
{\vec{\varphi}_{\!A{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}(t)=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}(t)}]_{|{\vec{A}}}=\sum_{i}x_{\!Ai}(t)[{\vec{A}}_{i}(t)]_{|{\vec{A}}}=\sum_{i}x_{\!Ai}(t){\vec{E}}_{i}
, thus
{\vec{\varphi}_{\!A{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}{}^{\prime}(t)=\sum_{i}x_{\!Ai}{}^{\prime}(t){\vec{E}}_{i}
.

Exercice 10.4

{\vec{u}}
is a
C^{1}
vector field,
p
is a point,
\vec{x}_{\!A}:=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}p}]_{|{\vec{A}}}
and

{\vec{u}}_{A}(\vec{x}_{\!A}):=[{\vec{u}}(p)]_{|{\vec{A}}}
(matrices). Prove:

d{{\vec{u}}_{A}}(\vec{x}_{\!A})=[d{\vec{u}}(p)]_{|{\vec{A}}}
(endomorphism in
{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}
), i.e.
d{{\vec{u}}_{A}}(\vec{x}_{\!A}).[{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}=[d{\vec{u}}(p).{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}
for all
{\vec{w}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
.

Answer
. The point
p{+}h{\vec{w}}\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
is referenced by A as

[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}p}+h{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}p}]_{|{\vec{A}}}+h[{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}=\vec{x}_{\!A}+h[{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}
.
Thus

d{{\vec{u}}_{A}}(\vec{x}_{\!A}).[{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{{{\vec{u}}_{A}}(\vec{x}_{\!A}{+}h[{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{A}}})-{{\vec{u}}_{A}}(\vec{x}_{\!A})\over h}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{[{\vec{u}}(p{+}h{\vec{w}})]_{|{\vec{A}}}-[{\vec{u}}(p)]_{|{\vec{A}}}\over h}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{[{\vec{u}}(p{+}h{\vec{w}})-{\vec{w}}(p)]_{|{\vec{A}}}\over h}=[\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{{\vec{u}}(p{+}h{\vec{w}})-{\vec{w}}(p)\over h}]_{|{\vec{A}}}=[d{\vec{u}}(p).{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}=[d{\vec{u}}(p)]_{|{\vec{A}}}.[{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}
, true for all
{\vec{w}}
.

Exercice 10.5

Call
Q_{t}
the transition matrix from
({\vec{A}}_{it})
to
({\vec{B}}_{it})
at
t
.
Prove
\vec{x}_{\!At}=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{O_{\!Bt}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}+Q_{t}.\vec{x}_{\!Bt}
.

Answer
.
\vec{x}_{\!At}=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{p_{t}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{O_{\!Bt}}}+\overrightarrow{{O_{\!Bt}}{p_{t}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{O_{\!Bt}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}+[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!Bt}}{p_{t}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}
,
and the change of basis formula gives
[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!Bt}}{p_{t}}}]_{|{\vec{B}}}=Q_{t}^{-1}.[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!Bt}}{p_{t}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}
.

10.1.4
Motion of
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}

Particular case
{\it O\!b\!j}={\it O\!b\!j}\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}
:
Its motion in the Universe, also called the motion of
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}
, is noted

{{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr[t_{1},t_{2}]\times{\it O\!b\!j}\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}&\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\cr(t,{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})&\rightarrow{q_{t}}={{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}(t,{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}).\crcr}}\,\right.

(10.13)

At
t
at
{q_{t}}={{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}(t,{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})
, the Eulerian velocities and accelerations of
{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}
are

{{\vec{v}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}(t,{q_{t}})={\partial{{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}\over\partial t}(t,{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})\quad\hbox{and}\quad{\vec{\gamma}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}(t,{q_{t}})={\partial^{2}{{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}\over\partial^{2}t}(t,{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}).

(10.14)

10.1.5
Quantification: Drive and static “motion” of
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}

The “drive motion”
{\vec{\varphi}_{\!D}}
, also called the motion of
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}
in
{{\cal R}_{\!A}}
, and the “static motion”
{\vec{\varphi}_{\!S}}
is the quantification of
{{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}
by A and by B:

{\vec{\varphi}_{\!D}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr[t_{1},t_{2}]\times{\it O\!b\!j}\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}&\rightarrow{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{A}\!)\cr(t,{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})&\rightarrow{\vec{\varphi}_{\!D}}(t,{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}):=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}(t,{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})}]_{|{\vec{A}}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\vec{y}_{\!D}(t)=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}q(t)}]_{|{\vec{A}}},\crcr}}\,\right.

(10.15)

{\vec{\varphi}_{\!S}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\it O\!b\!j}\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}&\rightarrow{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{B}\!)\cr{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}&\rightarrow{\vec{\varphi}_{\!S}}({Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}):=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}{{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}(t,{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})}]_{|{\vec{B}}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\vec{y}_{\!S}=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}q(t)}]_{|{\vec{B}}}.\crcr}}\,\right.

(10.16)

(
{\vec{\varphi}_{\!S}}
is independent of
t
since
{\it O\!b\!j}\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}
is fixed in
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}
.)

The drive velocity, also called the velocity of
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}
in
{{\cal R}_{\!A}}
, and static velocity of
{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}
are

{\vec{v}_{\!D}}(t,\vec{y}_{\!Dt}):=[{{\vec{v}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}(t,{q_{t}})]_{|{\vec{A}}}\quad\hbox{when}\quad\vec{y}_{\!Dt}:=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{q_{t}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}},

(10.17)

{\vec{v}_{\!S}}(t,\vec{y}_{\!S}):=[{{\vec{v}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}(t,{q_{t}})]_{|{\vec{B}}}=[\vec{0}]{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\vec{0}\hbox{ (null matrix)}.

(10.18)

And the drive and static accelerations are

{\vec{\gamma}_{D}}(t,\vec{y}_{\!Dt})=[{\vec{\gamma}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}(t,{q_{t}})]_{|{\vec{A}}}

and
{\vec{\gamma}_{S}}(t,\vec{y}_{\!S})=\vec{0}
.

Why introduce
{\vec{\varphi}_{\!S}}
(static)?

. You can’t confuse a particle
{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}

\vec{y}_{\!S}
or
\vec{y}_{\!Dt}
at
t
. And see (
10.19
).

10.2
The translator
\Theta_{t}

10.2.1
Definition of
{\Theta_{t}}

Definition 10.7

At
t
, the translator
{\Theta_{t}}:{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{B}\!)\rightarrow{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{A}\!)
is defined with (
10.15
)-(
10.16
) by:

\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}q(t)}]_{|{\vec{B}}}={\vec{\varphi}_{\!S}}({Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})=\vec{y}_{\!S}\hbox{ position of ${Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}$ in ${{\cal R}_{\!B}}$ (static), and}\cr&[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}q(t)}]_{|{\vec{A}}}={\vec{\varphi}_{\!Dt}}({Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})=\vec{y}_{\!Dt}\hbox{ position of ${Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}$ at $t$ in ${{\cal R}_{\!A}}$ (moving)}\cr}}\,\right\}\Longrightarrow\vec{y}_{\!Dt}={\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S}),

(10.19)

i.e.
{\Theta_{t}}
is the “inter-referential function at
t
” which translates the “matrix position”

\vec{y}_{\!S}={\vec{\varphi}_{\!S}}({Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}{{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}(t,{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})}]_{|{\vec{B}}}\in{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{B}\!)
(position of
{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}
as stored by B) to the “matrix position”

\vec{y}_{\!Dt}={\vec{\varphi}_{\!D}}(t,{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}(t,{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})}]_{|{\vec{A}}}\in{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{A}\!)
(position of
{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}
as stored by A).
So
{\Theta_{t}}
is defined by

\framebox{$\displaystyle{\vec{\varphi}_{\!Dt}}={\Theta_{t}}\circ{\vec{\varphi}_{\!S}}$}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\it O\!b\!j}\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}&\rightarrow{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{A}\!)\cr{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}&\rightarrow{\vec{\varphi}_{\!Dt}}({Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}):={\Theta_{t}}({\vec{\varphi}_{\!S}}({Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})),\crcr}}\,\right.

(10.20)

i.e. defined by

{\Theta_{t}}:={\vec{\varphi}_{\!Dt}}\circ{\vec{\varphi}_{\!S}}^{\,-1}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{B}\!)&\rightarrow{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{A}\!)\cr\vec{y}_{\!S}&\rightarrow\vec{y}_{\!Dt}={\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S}):={\vec{\varphi}_{\!Dt}}({\vec{\varphi}_{\!S}}^{\,-1}(\vec{y}_{\!S}))\crcr}}\,\right.

(10.21)

(stored position by B to stored position by A).

E.g., for
{Q_{\!{O_{\!B}}}}
the particle in
{\it O\!b\!j}\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}
at
t
at
{O_{\!Bt}}
(chosen by B to locate its origin), (
10.20
) gives, with
\vec{0}
the null matrix in
{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}
,

[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{O_{\!Bt}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}={\Theta_{t}}(\vec{0}).

(10.22)

So,
{\Theta_{t}}
is defined such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\qquad\vec{y}_{\!S}={\vec{\varphi}_{\!S}}({Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})=\hbox{localization of ${Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}$ by B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textstyle{\Theta_{t}}}
\textstyle{{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}\in{\it O\!b\!j}\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textstyle{\vec{\varphi}_{\!S}}\qquad}
\scriptstyle{\textstyle{\vec{\varphi}_{\!Dt}}\qquad}
\textstyle{\qquad\vec{y}_{\!Dt}={\vec{\varphi}_{\!Dt}}({Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})={\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S})=\hbox{localization at $t$ of ${Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}$ by A}.}

(10.23)

10.2.2
Translation at
t
for the motion
{\widetilde{\Phi}}

t
is fixed, the position
{p_{t}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
of a particle
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}\in{\it O\!b\!j}
is also the position
{q_{t}}={{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}(t,{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})
of a particle
{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}\in{\it O\!b\!j}\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}
, so
{\vec{\varphi}_{\!At}}({P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{p_{t}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{q_{t}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}={\vec{\varphi}_{\!Dt}}({Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})
,
and
{\vec{\varphi}_{\!Bt}}({P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}{p_{t}}}]_{|{\vec{B}}}=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}{q_{t}}}]_{|{\vec{B}}}={\vec{\varphi}_{\!S}}({Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})
, thus (
10.20
) gives

\framebox{$\displaystyle{\vec{\varphi}_{\!At}}={\Theta_{t}}\circ{\vec{\varphi}_{\!Bt}}$}.

(10.24)

10.3
d{\Theta_{t}}

10.3.1
Push-forward

If
\vec{y}_{\!S}\in{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{B}\!)
,
{\vec{w}_{S}}\in{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}
and
\vec{y}_{\!Dt}={\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S})
, then

{\vec{w}_{St*}}(\vec{y}_{\!Dt}):=d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S}).{\vec{w}_{S}}\quad(=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S}+h{{\vec{w}}_{B}})-{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S})\over h})

(10.25)

is the push-forward of the matrix
{\vec{w}_{S}}\in{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}
by
{\Theta_{t}}
.

So,
{\vec{w}_{St*}}([\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{q_{t}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}})=d{\Theta_{t}}([\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}{q_{t}}}]_{|{\vec{B}}}).[{\vec{w}}_{t}({q_{t}})]_{|{\vec{B}}}

for all
{q_{t}}\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
and all
{\vec{w}}_{t}:{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
(vector field).

10.3.2
{\Theta_{t}}
is affine in classical mechanics

Proposition 10.8

In
\mathbb{R}^{3}
(in classical mechanics),
{\Theta_{t}}
is affine:
For all
{Q_{\!B0}},{Q_{\!B1}}\in{\it O\!b\!j}\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}
and all
t,u\in\mathbb{R}
, with
{q_{t_{i}}}={{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}(t,{Q_{\!Bi}})\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
(positions at
t
in our Universe),

{\Theta_{t}}([\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}{q_{t_{0}}}}]_{|{\vec{B}}}+u\,[\overrightarrow{{q_{t_{0}}}{q_{t_{1}}}}]_{|{\vec{B}}})=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{q_{t_{0}}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}+u\,[\overrightarrow{{q_{t_{0}}}{q_{t_{1}}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}},\quad\hbox{and}\quad[\overrightarrow{{q_{t_{0}}}{q_{t_{1}}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}=d{\Theta_{t}}.[\overrightarrow{{q_{t_{0}}}{q_{t_{1}}}}]_{|{\vec{B}}}

(10.26)

the differential
d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S0}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}d{\Theta_{t}}
being independent of
\vec{y}_{\!S0}
.
In particular
[\vec{B}_{it}]_{{\vec{A}}}=d{\Theta_{t}}.[{\vec{B}}_{i}]_{|{\vec{B}}}
. In other words, for all
\vec{y}_{\!S0},\vec{y}_{\!S1}\in{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{B}\!)
and all
t,u\in\mathbb{R}
,

{\Theta_{t}}((1{-}u)\vec{y}_{\!S0}+u\,\vec{y}_{\!S1})=(1{-}u){\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S0})+u\,{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S1}),\quad\hbox{and}\quad{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S1})={\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S0})+d{\Theta_{t}}.(\vec{y}_{\!S1}{-}\vec{y}_{\!S0}).

(10.27)

Proof.

Consider the straight line (possible in classical mechanics in
{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
)

{q_{t}}:u\rightarrow{q_{t}}(u)={q_{t_{0}}}+u\,\overrightarrow{{q_{t_{0}}}{q_{t_{1}}}}\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
(fixed in
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}
),
in particular,
{q_{t}}(0)={q_{t_{0}}}
and
{q_{t}}(1)={q_{t_{1}}}
.
Let

\vec{y}_{\!S}(u)=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}{q_{t}}(u)}]_{|{\vec{B}}}
(positions stored by B), so

\vec{y}_{\!S}(u)=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}{q_{t_{0}}}}+u\,\overrightarrow{{q_{t_{0}}}{q_{t_{1}}}}]_{|{\vec{B}}}=[(1{-}u)\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}{q_{t_{0}}}}+u\,\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}{q_{t_{1}}}}]_{|{\vec{B}}}=(1{-}u)[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}{q_{t_{0}}}}]_{|{\vec{B}}}+u\,[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}{q_{t_{1}}}}]_{|{\vec{B}}}=(1{-}u)\vec{y}_{\!S0}+u\,\vec{y}_{\!S1}
, where
\vec{y}_{\!S0}=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}{q_{t_{0}}}}]_{|{\vec{B}}}=\vec{y}_{\!S}(0)
and
\vec{y}_{\!S1}=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}{q_{t_{1}}}}]_{|{\vec{B}}}=\vec{y}_{\!S}(1)
.
Idem for A:

\vec{y}_{\!Dt}(u)=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{q_{t}}(u)}]_{|{\vec{A}}}=(1{-}u){\vec{y}_{\!Dt_{0}}}+u{\vec{y}}_{Dt1}
(positions stored by A).
Thus

\displaystyle{(1{-}u){\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S0})+u{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S1})\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdefThetat0})}}(1{-}u){\vec{y}_{\!Dt_{0}}}+u{\vec{y}}_{Dt1}=\vec{y}_{\!Dt}(u)\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdefThetat0})}}{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S}(u))={\Theta_{t}}((1{-}u)\vec{y}_{\!S0}+u\vec{y}_{\!S1}),}

thus (
10.27
)
1
, thus (
10.26
)
1
.
Hence (derivation in
u
):

-{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S0})+{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S1})=d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S}(u)).(-\vec{y}_{\!S0}+\vec{y}_{\!S1})
,
true for all
u
, thus
d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S}(u))
is independent of
u
,
d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S}(u))=d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S0})
, true for all
\vec{y}_{\!S0}
, so
d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S0}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}d{\Theta_{t}}
, thus (
10.27
)
2
, thus (
10.26
)
2
.

Thus
[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!Bt}}{P_{\!Bti}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}=d{\Theta_{t}}.[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!Bt}}{P_{\!Bti}}}]_{|{\vec{B}}}
where
{P_{\!Bti}}
is s.t.
\vec{B}_{it}=\overrightarrow{{O_{\!Bt}}{P_{\!Bti}}}
, thus
[\vec{B}_{it}]_{{\vec{A}}}=d{\Theta_{t}}.[{\vec{B}}_{i}]_{|{\vec{B}}}
.

Exercice 10.9

Call
Q_{t}=[Q_{t,ij}]
the transition matrix from
(\vec{A}_{it})
to
(\vec{B}_{it})
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
,
and
({\vec{E}}_{i})
the canonical basis in
{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}
.
Prove

[d{\Theta_{t}}]_{|{\vec{E}}}=Q_{t},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad d{\Theta_{t}}.{\vec{E}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}Q_{t,ij}{\vec{E}}_{i},\;\;\forall j.

(10.28)

Answer
.
\vec{B}_{jt}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}Q_{t,ij}\vec{A}_{it}
gives

[\vec{B}_{jt}]_{|{\vec{A}}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}Q_{t,ij}{\vec{E}}_{i}
, and

[\vec{B}_{jt}]_{|{\vec{A}}}\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqvyDtnb0})}}d{\Theta_{t}}.[\vec{B}_{jt}]_{|{\vec{B}}}=d{\Theta_{t}}.{\vec{E}}_{j}
.

10.4
Translated velocities

t
is fixed,
{\vec{v}}_{t}({p_{t}})={\partial{\widetilde{\Phi}}\over\partial t}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
is the velocity of a particle
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}\in{\it O\!b\!j}
at
t
at
{p_{t}}
,

\vec{x}_{\!At}:=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!At}}{p_{t}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}
,
\vec{x}_{\!Bt}:=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!Bt}}{p_{t}}}]_{|{\vec{B}}}
, and
{\Theta_{t}}
affine.

Definition 10.10

The translated relative velocity and acceleration from B to A at
t
at
{p_{t}}
are the matrices

\framebox{$\displaystyle{{\vec{v}}_{Bt*}}(\vec{x}_{\!At}):=d{\Theta_{t}}.{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})$}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle{{\vec{\gamma}}_{Bt*}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})=d{\Theta_{t}}.{{\vec{\gamma}}_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})$}.

(10.29)

I.e.,
{{\vec{v}}_{Bt*}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})=d{\Theta_{t}}.[{\vec{v}}_{t}({p_{t}})]_{{\vec{B}}}
and
{{\vec{\gamma}}_{Bt*}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})=d{\Theta_{t}}.[{\vec{\gamma}}_{t}({p_{t}})]_{{\vec{B}}}
.

Interpretation:
Let
{q_{t_{0}}}
and
{q_{t_{1}}}
be particles in
{\it O\!b\!j}\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}
s.t.
{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})=[\overrightarrow{{q_{t_{0}}}{q_{t_{1}}}}]_{|{\vec{B}}}
where
\vec{x}_{\!Bt}=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}{q_{t_{0}}}}]_{|{\vec{B}}}

(here
\overrightarrow{{q_{t_{0}}}{q_{t_{1}}}}
is a tangent vector at
{q_{t_{0}}}
to the curve
{q_{t}}:u\rightarrow{q_{t}}(u)={q_{t_{0}}}+u\,\overrightarrow{{q_{t_{0}}}{q_{t_{1}}}}
in the proof of prop.
10.8
); Then
[\overrightarrow{{q_{t_{0}}}{q_{t_{1}}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqvyDtnb0})}}d{\Theta_{t}}.[\overrightarrow{{q_{t_{0}}}{q_{t_{1}}}}]_{|{\vec{B}}}
gives
[\overrightarrow{{q_{t_{0}}}{q_{t_{1}}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}=d{\Theta_{t}}.{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})={{\vec{v}}_{Bt*}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})
.
Similarly for
{{\vec{\gamma}}_{Bt*}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})
.

Exercice 10.11

({\vec{A}}_{i})
and
({\vec{B}}_{i})
are Euclidean basis (e.g. in foot and metre),

(\cdot,\cdot)_{A}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{B}
are the associated Euclidean dot products,
\lambda=||{\vec{B}}_{i}||_{A}
(e.g.
\simeq 3.28
),

({\vec{E}}_{i})
is the canonical basis in
{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}
, and

(\cdot,\cdot)_{\cal M}
is the canonical inner dot product in
{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}
.
Call
\vec{E}_{it*}:=d{\Theta_{t}}.{\vec{E}}_{i}
and prove:

\forall i,j,\;\;(\vec{E}_{it*},\vec{E}_{jt*})_{\cal M}=\lambda^{2}\delta_{ij},\quad\hbox{and}\quad d{\Theta_{t}}^{T}.d{\Theta_{t}}=\lambda^{2}I.

(10.30)

Answer
.
(\vec{B}_{it})
is a Euclidean basis for B, thus is a Euclidean orthogonal basis for all observers, in particular for A, with

||\vec{B}_{it}||_{A}=\lambda
for all
i
. And
\vec{E}_{it*}=d{\Theta_{t}}.[\vec{B}_{jt}]_{|{\vec{B}}}\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqvyDtnb0})}}[\vec{B}_{it}]_{|{\vec{A}}}
.
Thus

(\vec{E}_{it*},\vec{E}_{jt*})_{\cal M}=[\vec{E}_{it*}]^{T}.[\vec{E}_{jt*}]=[\vec{B}_{it}]_{|{\vec{A}}}^{T}.[\vec{B}_{jt}]_{|{\vec{A}}}=(\vec{B}_{it},\vec{B}_{jt})_{A}=\lambda^{2}(\vec{B}_{it},\vec{B}_{jt})_{B}=\lambda^{2}\delta_{ij}
, thus (
10.30
)
1
;
Then

\lambda^{2}\delta_{ij}=(d{\Theta_{t}}.{\vec{E}}_{i},d{\Theta_{t}}.{\vec{E}}_{j})_{\cal M}=(d{\Theta_{t}}^{T}.d{\Theta_{t}}.{\vec{E}}_{i},{\vec{E}}_{j})_{\cal M}
, true for all
i,j
,
thus
d{\Theta_{t}}^{T}.d{\Theta_{t}}=\lambda^{2}I
, thus (
10.30
)
2
.

10.5
Definition of
\Theta

Definition 10.12

The translator from B to A is the function
\Theta

defined with (
10.20
) by

\Theta:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\bigcup_{t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]}(\{t\}\times{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{B}\!))&\rightarrow{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{A}\!)\cr(t,\vec{y}_{\!S})&\rightarrow\framebox{$\displaystyle\Theta(t,\vec{y}_{\!S}):={\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S})$},\crcr}}\,\right.

(10.31)

i.e., for all
{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}\in{\it O\!b\!j}\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}
and all
t
,

\Theta(t,[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}{{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}(t,{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})}]_{|{\vec{B}}})=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}(t,{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})}]_{|{\vec{A}}},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad\Theta(t,{\vec{\varphi}_{\!S}}({Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}))={\vec{\varphi}_{\!D}}(t,{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}).

(10.32)

E.g., (
10.22
) gives

\Theta(t,\vec{0})=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{O_{\!B}}(t)}]_{|{\vec{A}}}
.

Remark 10.13

The translator
\Theta
looks like a motion, but is not: A “usual” motion is defined by one observer and connects one particle to its position; While
\Theta
connects two “matrix positions” of one particle relative to two referentials:

\Theta
is an “inter-referential” function.

10.6
The “
\Theta
-velocity” is the drive velocity

Definition 10.14

The “
\Theta
-velocity” and “
\Theta
-acceleration” are defined by (Eulerian type definition)

\hbox{with}\quad\vec{y}_{\!Dt}=\Theta(t,\vec{y}_{\!S}),\quad\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&{{\vec{v}}_{\Theta}}(t,\vec{y}_{\!Dt}):={\partial\Theta\over\partial t}(t,\vec{y}_{\!S})\;\;(\in{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}),\cr&{\vec{\gamma}}_{\Theta}(t,\vec{y}_{\!Dt})={\partial^{2}\Theta\over\partial t^{2}}(t,\vec{y}_{\!S})\;\;(\in{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}).\crcr}}\,\right.

(10.33)

Proposition 10.15

\framebox{$\displaystyle{{\vec{v}}_{\Theta}}={\vec{v}_{\!D}}$}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle{\vec{\gamma}}_{\Theta}={\vec{\gamma}}_{D}$},

(10.34)

i.e.
{{\vec{v}}_{\Theta}}(t,{\vec{y}})={\vec{v}_{\!D}}(t,{\vec{y}})
and
{\vec{\gamma}}_{\Theta}(t,{\vec{y}})={\vec{\gamma}}_{D}(t,{\vec{y}})
in
{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}
, for all
t
and all
{\vec{y}}\in{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{A}\!)
.

Proof.
\Theta(t,{\vec{\varphi}_{\!S}}({Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}))={\vec{\varphi}_{\!D}}(t,{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})
gives

{\partial\Theta\over\partial t}(t,{\vec{\varphi}_{\!S}}({Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}))={\partial{\vec{\varphi}_{\!D}}\over\partial t}(t,{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}),\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad{{\vec{v}}_{\Theta}}(t,\Theta(t,{\vec{\varphi}_{\!S}}({Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})))={\vec{v}_{\!D}}(t,{\vec{\varphi}_{\!D}}(t,{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})),

(10.35)

thus
{{\vec{v}}_{\Theta}}(t,{\vec{\varphi}_{\!D}}(t,{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}))={\vec{v}_{\!D}}(t,{\vec{\varphi}_{\!D}}(t,{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}))
, thus

{{\vec{v}}_{\Theta}}(t,{\vec{y}})={\vec{v}_{\!D}}(t,{\vec{y}})
for all
{\vec{y}}\in{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{A}\!)
.
Idem with
{\partial^{2}\over\partial t^{2}}
.

10.7
The velocity-addition formula

(
10.24
) gives

{\vec{\varphi}_{\!A}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})=\Theta(t,{\vec{\varphi}_{\!B}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})),

(10.36)

thus

{\partial{\vec{\varphi}_{\!A}}\over\partial t}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})={\partial\Theta\over\partial t}(t,{\vec{\varphi}_{\!B}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}))+d\Theta(t,{\vec{\varphi}_{\!B}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})).{\partial{\vec{\varphi}_{\!B}}\over\partial t}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}).

(10.37)

Thus

{{\vec{v}}_{\!A}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!At})={{\vec{v}}_{\Theta}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!At})+d\Theta(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{{\vec{v}}_{\!B}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt}),

(10.38)

where

\vec{x}_{\!Bt}={\vec{\varphi}_{\!B}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
and
\vec{x}_{\!At}={\vec{\varphi}_{\!A}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})={\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})
.
Thus, with
{{\vec{v}}_{\Theta}}\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqiwt})}}{\vec{v}_{\!D}}
,

\framebox{$\displaystyle{{\vec{v}}_{\!At}}={{\vec{v}}_{Bt*}}+{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}}$}\quad\hbox{where}\quad{{\vec{v}}_{Bt*}}(\vec{x}_{\!At}):=d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}),

(10.39)

which is the velocity-addition formula in
{{\cal R}_{\!A}}
:

\displaystyle{\hbox{${{\vec{v}}_{\!At}}$ the absolute velocity}=}

{{\vec{v}}_{Bt*}}
the translated relative velocity from B to A

\displaystyle{{}+\hbox{${{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}}$ the drive velocity}.}

(10.40)

In other words (relation between the numerical values of the velocities stored by A and B),

[{\vec{v}}_{t}({p_{t}})]_{|{\vec{A}}}=d{\Theta_{t}}.[{\vec{v}}_{t}({p_{t}})]_{{\vec{B}}}+[{{\vec{v}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!Bt}}}({p_{t}})]_{|{\vec{A}}}.

(10.41)

10.8
Coriolis acceleration, and the acceleration-addition formula

(
10.37
) gives

\displaystyle{{\partial^{2}{\vec{\varphi}_{\!A}}\over\partial t^{2}}}

\displaystyle{{}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})={\partial^{2}\Theta\over\partial t^{2}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt})+d{\partial\Theta\over\partial t}(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{\partial{\vec{\varphi}_{\!B}}\over\partial t}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})}

\displaystyle{{}+\bigl({\partial(d\Theta)\over\partial t}(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt})+d^{2}\Theta(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{\partial{\vec{\varphi}_{\!B}}\over\partial t}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})\bigr).{\partial{\vec{\varphi}_{\!B}}\over\partial t}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})+d\Theta(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{\partial^{2}{\vec{\varphi}_{\!B}}\over\partial t^{2}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}).}

(10.42)

And
d^{2}{\Theta_{t}}=0
in our classical framework (
{\Theta_{t}}
is affine);
And
{\partial\Theta\over\partial t}(t,\vec{y}_{\!S})={{\vec{v}}_{\Theta t}}({\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S}))
gives

{\partial(d\Theta)\over\partial t}(t,\vec{y}_{\!S})=d({\partial\Theta\over\partial t})(t,\vec{y}_{\!S})=d{{\vec{v}}_{\Theta t}}({\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S})).d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S})
;
And
{\partial{\vec{\varphi}_{\!B}}\over\partial t}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})={{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})
where
\vec{x}_{\!Bt}={\vec{\varphi}_{\!B}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
; Thus

\displaystyle{{\vec{\gamma}}_{At}(\vec{x}_{\!At})=}

\displaystyle{{}{\vec{\gamma}}_{\Theta t}(\vec{x}_{\!At})+2d{{\vec{v}}_{\Theta t}}(\vec{x}_{\!At}).d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})+d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{\vec{\gamma}}_{Bt}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}{{\vec{\gamma}}_{Dt}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})+2d{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}}(\vec{x}_{\!At}).{{\vec{v}}_{Bt*}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})+{{\vec{\gamma}}_{Bt*}}(\vec{x}_{\!At}).}

(10.43)

Definition 10.16

At
t
, the Coriolis acceleration
{{\vec{\gamma}}_{Ct}}
at
\vec{x}_{\!At}
is

{{\vec{\gamma}}_{Ct}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})=2d{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}}(\vec{x}_{\!At}).{{\vec{v}}_{Bt*}}(\vec{x}_{\!At}),\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle{\vec{\gamma}}_{Ct}=2d{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}}.{{\vec{v}}_{Bt*}}$}.

(10.44)

And the Coriolis acceleration
{{\vec{\gamma}}_{C}}
at
t
at
\vec{x}_{\!At}
is
{{\vec{\gamma}}_{C}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!At}):={{\vec{\gamma}}_{Ct}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})
.

Thus (
10.43
) gives the acceleration-addition formula in
{{\cal R}_{\!A}}
:

\framebox{$\displaystyle{\vec{\gamma}}_{At}={\vec{\gamma}}_{Bt*}+{{\vec{\gamma}}_{Dt}}+{\vec{\gamma}}_{Ct}$},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad

(10.45)

\displaystyle{\hbox{${\vec{\gamma}}_{At}$ the absolute acceleration}=}

{\vec{\gamma}}_{Bt*}
the translated relative acceleration from B to A

\displaystyle{{}+\hbox{${{\vec{\gamma}}_{Dt}}$ the drive acceleration}+\hbox{${\vec{\gamma}}_{Ct}$ the Coriolis acceleration}.}

(10.46)

In other words (relation between the numerical values of the acceletations stored by A and B),

[{\vec{\gamma}}_{t}({p_{t}})]_{|{\vec{A}}}=d{\Theta_{t}}.[{\vec{\gamma}}_{t}({p_{t}})]_{{\vec{B}}}+[{\vec{\gamma}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!Bt}}}({p_{t}})]_{|{\vec{A}}}+2[d{{\vec{v}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!Bt}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}.d{\Theta_{t}}.[{\vec{v}}_{t}({p_{t}})]_{|{\vec{B}}}.

(10.47)

10.9
With an initial time

Let
{t_{0}},t\in\mathbb{R}
.
Consider the Lagrangian associated function
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
with the motion
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
of
{\it O\!b\!j}
:

{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\Omega_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow{\Omega_{t}}\cr{p_{t_{0}}}{=}{\widetilde{\Phi}}({t_{0}},{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})&\rightarrow{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}):={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}).\crcr}}\,\right.

(10.48)

And, with
\vec{x}_{\!At}={\vec{\varphi}_{\!A}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{p_{t}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}
and
\vec{x}_{\!Bt}={\vec{\varphi}_{\!B}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}{p_{t}}}]_{|{\vec{B}}}
, define the “matrix motions”
{\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!At}}:{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{A}\!)\rightarrow{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{A}\!)
and
{\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!Bt}}:{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{B}\!)\rightarrow{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{B}\!)
by

\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&{\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At_{0}}):=\vec{x}_{\!At}\quad(=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})}]_{|{\vec{A}}}=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})}]_{|{\vec{A}}}={\vec{\varphi}_{\!At}}({P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})),\cr&{\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt_{0}}):=\vec{x}_{\!Bt}\quad(=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}{\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})}]_{|{\vec{B}}}=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})}]_{|{\vec{B}}}={\vec{\varphi}_{\!Bt}}({P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})).\cr}}\,\right.

(10.49)

And
{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})=\vec{x}_{\!At}
, i.e.
{\Theta_{t}}({\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt_{0}}))={\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At_{0}})

with
\vec{x}_{\!At_{0}}={\Theta_{t_{0}}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt_{0}})
, thus

\framebox{$\displaystyle{\Theta_{t}}\circ{\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!Bt}}={\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!At}}\circ{\Theta_{t_{0}}}$}:{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{B}\!)\rightarrow{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{A}\!).

(10.50)

In other words, the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\vec{x}_{\!Bt_{0}}={\vec{\varphi}_{\!B}}({t_{0}},{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textstyle{\Theta_{t_{0}}}}
\scriptstyle{\textstyle{\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!Bt}}}
\textstyle{\vec{x}_{\!Bt}={\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt_{0}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textstyle{\Theta_{t}}}
\textstyle{{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}\in{\it O\!b\!j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textstyle{\vec{\varphi}_{\!Bt_{0}}}}
\scriptstyle{\textstyle{\vec{\varphi}_{\!At_{0}}}}
\textstyle{\vec{x}_{\!At_{0}}={\vec{\varphi}_{\!A}}({t_{0}},{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})={\Theta_{t_{0}}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt_{0}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\;\textstyle{\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!At}}}
\textstyle{\vec{x}_{\!At}={\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At_{0}})={\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).}

(10.51)

Thus, for any vector field
{{\vec{u}}_{B{t_{0}}}}
in
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}
,

\underbrace{d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})}_{\hbox{\footnotesize(translation at t)}}.\underbrace{d{\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt_{0}}).{{\vec{u}}_{B{t_{0}}}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt_{0}})}_{\hbox{\footnotesize(deformation from ${t_{0}}$ to t)}}=\underbrace{d{\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At_{0}})}_{\hbox{\footnotesize(deformation from ${t_{0}}$ to t)}}.\underbrace{d{\Theta_{t_{0}}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt_{0}}).{{\vec{u}}_{B{t_{0}}}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt_{0}})}_{\hbox{\footnotesize(translation at ${t_{0}}$)}}.

(10.52)

Exercice 10.17

Redo the above steps with
{\it O\!b\!j}\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}
instead of
{\it O\!b\!j}
.

Answer
. Consider the Lagrangian associated function
\Phi_{\!R\!Bt}^{t_{0}}
with the motion
{{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}
of
{\it O\!b\!j}\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}
:

\Phi_{\!R\!Bt}^{t_{0}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\Omega_{\!R\!B{t_{0}}}={\mathbb{R}^{n}}&\rightarrow\Omega_{\!R\!Bt}={\mathbb{R}^{n}}\cr{q_{t_{0}}}={{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}({t_{0}},{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})&\rightarrow{q_{t}}=\Phi_{\!R\!Bt}^{t_{0}}({q_{t_{0}}}):={{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}(t,{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}),\crcr}}\,\right\}

(10.53)

then define the “matrix motions”
{\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!Dt}}:{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{A}\!)\rightarrow{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{A}\!)
and
{\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!St}}:{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{B}\!)\rightarrow{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{B}\!)
by

\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&{\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!Dt}}({\vec{y}_{\!Dt_{0}}}):=\vec{y}_{\!Dt}\hskip 9.24994pt(=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}(t,{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})}]_{|{\vec{A}}}=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}\Phi_{\!R\!Bt}^{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}})}]_{|{\vec{A}}}={\vec{\varphi}_{\!Dt}}({Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})),\cr&{\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!St}}(\vec{y}_{\!S}):=\vec{y}_{\!S}\hskip 9.24994pt(=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}{{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}(t,{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})}]_{|{\vec{B}}}=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}\Phi_{\!R\!Bt}^{t_{0}}({q_{t_{0}}})}]_{|{\vec{B}}}={\vec{\varphi}_{\!S}}({Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})),\cr}}\,\right.

(10.54)

Thus
{\vec{\varphi}_{\!S}}
is a time-shift, which is also abusively noted
{\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!St}}=I
(algebraic identity).
So with
{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S})=\vec{y}_{\!Dt}
we get
{\Theta_{t}}({\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!Dt}}(\vec{y}_{\!S}))={\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!Dt}}({\vec{y}_{\!Dt_{0}}})
,
with
{\vec{y}_{\!Dt_{0}}}={\Theta_{t_{0}}}(\vec{y}_{\!S})
, thus

\framebox{$\displaystyle{\Theta_{t}}\circ{\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!St}}={\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!Dt}}\circ{\Theta_{t_{0}}}$}:{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{B}\!)\rightarrow{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{A}\!)

(10.55)

(also abusively written
{\Theta_{t}}={\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!Dt}}\circ{\Theta_{t_{0}}}
).
In other words, the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\vec{y}_{\!S}={\vec{\varphi}_{\!S}}({Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textstyle{\Theta_{t_{0}}}}
\scriptstyle{\textstyle{\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!St}}=\hbox{time shift}}
\textstyle{\vec{y}_{\!S}={\vec{\varphi}_{\!S}}({Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textstyle{\Theta_{t}}}
\textstyle{{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}\in{\it O\!b\!j}\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textstyle{\vec{\varphi}_{\!S}}\hskip 9.24994pt}
\scriptstyle{\textstyle{\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!D}}\hskip 9.24994pt}
\textstyle{{\vec{y}_{\!Dt_{0}}}={\vec{\varphi}_{\!Dt_{0}}}({Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})={\Theta_{t_{0}}}(\vec{y}_{\!S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textstyle{\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!Dt}}\hskip 18.49988pt\;\;}
\textstyle{\vec{y}_{\!Dt}={\vec{\varphi}_{\!Dt}}({Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})={\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!Dt}}({\vec{y}_{\!Dt_{0}}})={\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S}).}

(10.56)

And (
10.55
) gives, for any
\vec{y}_{\!S}={\vec{\varphi}_{\!S}}({Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})
and all vector field
{\vec{u}}_{\!S}
(static in
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}
),
with
{\vec{y}_{\!Dt_{0}}}={\Theta_{t_{0}}}(\vec{y}_{\!S})
,

\underbrace{d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S})}_{\hbox{\footnotesize(translation at t)}}.\underbrace{d{\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!St}}(\vec{y}_{\!S}).{\vec{u}}_{\!S}(\vec{y}_{\!S})}_{\hbox{\footnotesize(time shift from ${t_{0}}$ to t)}}=\underbrace{d{\vec{\varphi}^{t_{0}}_{\!Dt}}({\vec{y}_{\!Dt_{0}}})}_{\hbox{\footnotesize(Drive motion from ${t_{0}}$ to t)}}.\underbrace{d{\Theta_{t_{0}}}(\vec{y}_{\!S}).{\vec{u}}_{\!S}(\vec{y}_{\!S})}_{\hbox{\footnotesize(translation at ${t_{0}}$)}}.

(10.57)

10.10
Drive and Coriolis forces

10.10.1
Fundamental principal: requires a Galilean referential

Second Newton’s law of motion (fundamental principle of dynamics):
In a Galilean referential,
the sum of the external forces
{\vec{f}}

on an object is equal to its mass multiplied by its acceleration:

\sum\hbox{external}{\vec{f}}=m{\vec{\gamma}}\quad\hbox{(in a Galilean referential)}.

(10.58)

Question: And in a Non Galilean referential?

Answer: Then you have to add “observer dependent forces”,
i.e. you have to add “apparent forces” due to the motion of the non Galilean observer.
Indeed, the motion of an object in our Universe does not care about the observer motion (his accelerations and velocities).

See e.g.
https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_36MiCUS1ro
for a carousel (a merry-go-round),

See e.g.
https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=aeY9tY9vKgs
for tornadoes.

10.10.2
Drive + Coriolis forces = the inertial force

Consider

{\vec{f}}(t,{p_{t}})=
the sum of the external forces acting on
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
at
t
at
{p_{t}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
.

In a Galilean referential
{{\cal R}_{\!A}}
, Newton laws (
10.58
) means

[{\vec{f}}_{t}({p_{t}})]_{|{\vec{A}}}=m\,[{\vec{\gamma}}_{t}({p_{t}})]_{|{\vec{A}}},\quad\hbox{written}\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle{{\vec{f}}_{At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})=m\,{{\vec{\gamma}}_{At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})$}\;\in{\cal M}_{\!n\!1},

(10.59)

with
\vec{x}_{\!At}:=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{p_{t}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}
,
{{\vec{f}}_{At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At}):=[{\vec{f}}_{t}({p_{t}})]_{|{\vec{A}}}
and
{{\vec{\gamma}}_{At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})=[{\vec{\gamma}}_{t}({p_{t}})]_{|{\vec{A}}}
.
With
\vec{x}_{\!At}={\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})
, the acceleration addition formula gives

{{\vec{f}}_{At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})=m(d{\Theta_{t}}.{{\vec{\gamma}}_{B}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})+{{\vec{\gamma}}_{Dt}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})+{{\vec{\gamma}}_{Ct}}(\vec{x}_{\!At}))\in{{\cal R}_{\!A}}
, thus, in
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}
,

\underbrace{d{\Theta_{t}}^{-1}.{{\vec{f}}_{At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})}_{{{\vec{f}}_{At}}{}^{*}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})={{\vec{f}}_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})}=m\,{{\vec{\gamma}}_{B}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})+\underbrace{m\,d{\Theta_{t}}^{-1}.{\vec{\gamma}}_{Dt}(\vec{x}_{\!At})}_{m\,{{\vec{\gamma}}_{Dt}}{}^{*}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})}+\underbrace{m\,d{\Theta_{t}}^{-1}.{\vec{\gamma}}_{Ct}(\vec{x}_{\!At})}_{m\,{{\vec{\gamma}}_{Ct}}{}^{*}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})},

(10.60)

and
d{\Theta_{t}}^{-1}.[{\vec{f}}_{t}({p_{t}})]_{|{\vec{A}}}=d{\Theta_{t}}^{-1}.{{\vec{f}}_{At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqvyDtnb0})}}[{\vec{f}}_{t}({p_{t}})]_{|{\vec{B}}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{{\vec{f}}_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})
is the external forces as quantified by B at
t
, cf. (
10.26
) (with
{\Theta_{t}}
supposed to be affine).
And with the pull-back notation, cf. (
10.26
):

Definition 10.18

At
t
on
{p_{t}}
, define

\displaystyle{\bullet}

\displaystyle{{}\hbox{The drive force }\hfill{\vec{f}_{\!B\!Dt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}):=-m\,d{\Theta_{t}}^{-1}.{\vec{\gamma}}_{Dt}(\vec{x}_{\!At})\quad(=-m\,{{\vec{\gamma}}_{Dt}}{}^{*}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})).}

\displaystyle{\bullet}

\displaystyle{{}\hbox{The Coriolis force }\hfill{\vec{f}_{\!B\!Ct}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}):=-m\,d{\Theta_{t}}^{-1}.{\vec{\gamma}}_{Ct}(\vec{x}_{\!At})\quad(=-m\,{{\vec{\gamma}}_{Ct}}{}^{*}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})).}

\displaystyle{\bullet}

\displaystyle{{}(\hbox{The inertial, or fictitious, force}:={\vec{f}_{\!B\!Dt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})+{\vec{f}_{\!B\!Ct}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})=-m\,d{\Theta_{t}}^{-1}.({\vec{\gamma}}_{Dt}+{\vec{\gamma}}_{Ct})(\vec{x}_{\!At}).)}

(10.61)

Then (
10.60
) gives the fundamental principle quantified in
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}
(non Galilean referential):

\framebox{$\displaystyle{{\vec{f}}_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})+{\vec{f}_{\!B\!Dt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})+{\vec{f}_{\!B\!Ct}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})=m\,{{\vec{\gamma}}_{B}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})$},

(10.62)

i.e., at
t
, in
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}
: The external force
+
the Drive and Coriolis forces
=

m
times the acceleration.

10.11
Summary for “Sun and Earth”

Illustation with a simplified (circular) motion of the Earth around the Sun.

10.11.1
Coriolis forces on the Earth

1.

Referentials.

1.1.

Relative referential
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}=({O_{\!B}},({\vec{B}}_{1},{\vec{B}}_{2},{\vec{B}}_{3}))
chosen by the observer B fixed on the Earth,
where
{O_{\!Bt}}={{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}(t,{Q_{\!{O_{\!B}}}})
is the position of the particle
{Q_{\!{O_{\!B}}}}
at the center of the Earth,
written
{O_{\!B}}
by B (fixed for B), and

({\vec{B}}_{1t},{\vec{B}}_{2t},{\vec{B}}_{3t})
is a Euclidean basis (e.g. built with the metre) fixed in the Earth,
written
({\vec{B}}_{1},{\vec{B}}_{2},{\vec{B}}_{3})
by B (fixed for B),
with
{\vec{B}}_{3}
chosen to be along the rotation axis of the Earth and oriented from the south pole to the north pole;
And
(\cdot,\cdot)_{B}
is the associated Euclidean dot product.
So, a fixed particle
{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}
in the Earth at
longitude
\theta_{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}\in]-\pi,\pi]
and latitude
\varphi_{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}\in[-{\pi\over 2},{\pi\over 2}]

is referenced by observer B as the matrix

\vec{y}_{\!S}={\vec{\varphi}_{\!S}}({Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}{{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}(t,{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})}]_{|{\vec{B}}}=R_{\!B}\pmatrix{\cos(\theta_{\!{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}})\cos(\varphi_{\!{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}})\cr\sin(\theta_{\!{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}})\cos(\varphi_{\!{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}})\cr\sin(\varphi_{\!{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}})\hfill}
where
R_{\!B}=||\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}{{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}(t,{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})}||_{B}
is the distance between
{Q_{\!{O_{\!B}}}}
and
{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}

(e.g. if
{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}
is on the surface of the Earth then
R_{\!B}\simeq 6371
km).

1.2.

Initial Galilean referential

{{\cal R}_{\!A}}_{0}=(O_{\!A0},({\vec{A}}_{1},{\vec{A}}_{2},{\vec{A}}_{3}))
:
O_{\!A0}
is at the center of the Sun and
({\vec{A}}_{1},{\vec{A}}_{2},{\vec{A}}_{3})
is a Euclidean basis (e.g. built with the foot) fixed relative to the stars, such that
{\vec{A}}_{3}=\mu{\vec{B}}_{3}
with
\mu>0
(e.g.
\mu=0.3048
and
\lambda={1\over\mu}\simeq 3.28
); And
(\cdot,\cdot)_{A}
is the associated Euclidean dot product.

1.3.

Deduced absolute Galilean referential
{{\cal R}_{\!A}}=({O_{\!At}},({\vec{A}}_{1},{\vec{A}}_{2},{\vec{A}}_{3}))
chosen by observer A fixed on Earth,
where
{O_{\!At}}={O_{\!Bt}}
, written
{O_{\!A}}
by A (fixed for A).
Since it takes more that 365 days for
{Q_{\!{O_{\!B}}}}
to complete a rotation around the Sun, the motion of
{Q_{\!{O_{\!B}}}}
will be considered to be rectilinear at constant velocity “in a short interval of time”
sufficient for the computation of the Coriolis acceleration with “sufficient accuracy” (simplifies the calculations).

(If A prefers to work with the initial Galilean referential
{{\cal R}_{\!A}}_{0}
, then the absolute matrix motion
{\vec{\varphi}_{\!A}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})}]_{|{\vec{A}}}
has to be replaced by
{\vec{\varphi}_{\!A}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})=[\overrightarrow{O_{\!A0}{O_{\!B}}(t)}]_{|{\vec{A}}}+[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}(t){\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})}]_{|{\vec{A}}}
, idem for the drive motion
{\vec{\varphi}_{\!D}}
.)

2.

Drive motion.

2.1.

The motion
t\rightarrow{q_{t}}={{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}(t,{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})
of a particle
{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}
in the Earth is stored by A as the drive motion
{\vec{\varphi}_{\!D}}
given by (matrix valued), with
\omega
the angular velocity of the Earth in
{{\cal R}_{\!A}}
,

\vec{y}_{\!D}(t)={\vec{\varphi}_{\!D}}(t,{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})=R_{\!A}({Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})\pmatrix{\cos(\omega t)\cos\varphi_{\!{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}}\cr\sin(\omega t)\cos\varphi_{\!{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}}\cr\sin\varphi_{\!{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}}\hfill}=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}q(t)}]_{|{\vec{A}}}=\pmatrix{y_{D1}(t)\cr y_{D2}(t)\cr y_{D3}},

(10.63)

where
R_{\!A}({Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}})=||\overrightarrow{{Q_{\!{O_{\!B}}}}{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}}||_{|{\vec{A}}}
is the distance between
{Q_{\!{O_{\!B}}}}
and
{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}
for A (e.g.
R_{\!A}\simeq 20902231
foot if
{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}
is on the surface of the Earth).
(And
(\omega t)
by replaced by
(\alpha_{0}{+}\omega(t{-}{t_{0}}))
to be more general.)

2.2.

Drive velocity: With
{\vec{\omega}_{\!D}}:=\omega{\vec{A}}_{3}
,

{\vec{v}_{\!D}}(t,\vec{y}_{\!D}(t))=\vec{y}_{\!D}\!^{\prime}(t)=\omega R_{\!A}\pmatrix{-\sin(\omega t)\cos\varphi_{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}\cr\cos(\omega t)\cos\varphi_{Q_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}\hfill\cr 0\hfill}=\omega\pmatrix{-y_{2}(t)\cr y_{1}(t)\cr 0}=\omega\pmatrix{0&-1&0\cr 1&0&0\cr 0&0&0}.\vec{y}_{\!D}(t)={\vec{\omega}_{\!D}}\wedge\vec{y}_{\!D}(t).

(10.64)

2.3.

Drive acceleration:

{\vec{\gamma}_{D}}(t,\vec{y}_{\!Dt})=\vec{y}_{\!D}\!^{\prime\prime}(t)={\vec{\omega}_{\!D}}\wedge\vec{y}_{\!D}\!^{\prime}(t)={\vec{\omega}_{\!D}}\wedge{\vec{v}_{\!D}}(t,\vec{y}_{\!Dt})={\vec{\omega}_{\!D}}\wedge({\vec{\omega}_{\!D}}\wedge\vec{y}_{\!D}(t))=-\omega^{2}\pmatrix{y_{D1}(t)\cr y_{D2}(t)\cr 0}

(10.65)

= the usual centrifugal acceleration (in a plane parallel to the equatorial plane, drawing).

2.4.

Differential of the drive velocity (time and space independent here):
(
10.64
) gives

d{\vec{v}_{\!D}}(t,\vec{y}_{\!Dt})=d{\vec{v}_{\!D}}=\pmatrix{0&-\omega&0\cr\omega&0&0\cr 0&0&0}={\vec{\omega}_{\!D}}\wedge.

(10.66)

3.

Translator.

3.1.

Here
{O_{\!At}}={O_{\!Bt}}
, thus
{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{0})=\vec{0}
(with
[\vec{0}]{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\vec{0}=
the null matrix), cf. (
10.22
).

3.2.

Calculation of
d{\Theta_{t}}
.
With
{\Theta_{t}}
affine,
d{\Theta_{t}}.[\vec{B}_{it}]_{|{\vec{B}}}=[\vec{B}_{it}]_{|{\vec{A}}}
.
Thus
{\vec{B}}_{3}=\lambda{\vec{A}}_{3}
(hypothesis) and
d{\Theta_{t}}.[{\vec{B}}_{3}]_{|{\vec{B}}}=[{\vec{B}}_{3t}]_{|{\vec{A}}}
give

d{\Theta_{t}}.{\vec{E}}_{3}=\lambda{\vec{E}}_{3}
where
({\vec{E}}_{i})
is the canonical basis in
{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}
.
Then let
{Q_{\!Bi}}\in{\it O\!b\!j}\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}
be the Earth particle which position
q_{ti}={{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}(t,{Q_{\!Bi}})
makes
\vec{B}_{it}:=\overrightarrow{{O_{\!Bt}}q_{ti}}
.

So,
{\vec{B}}_{1}
and
{\vec{B}}_{2}
being in the equatorial plane, (
10.63
) gives

d{\Theta_{t}}.{\vec{E}}_{1}=d{\Theta_{t}}.[{\vec{B}}_{1}]_{|{\vec{B}}}=[{\vec{B}}_{1}]_{|{\vec{A}}}=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{q_{t1}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}=\lambda\pmatrix{\cos(\omega t)\cr\sin(\omega t)\cr 0}
,
and

d{\Theta_{t}}.{\vec{E}}_{2}=d{\Theta_{t}}.[{\vec{B}}_{2}]_{|{\vec{B}}}=[{\vec{B}}_{2}]_{|{\vec{A}}}=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{q_{t2}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}=\lambda\pmatrix{-\sin(\omega t)\cr\cos(\omega t)\cr 0}
.
Thus
[d{\Theta_{t}}]_{|{\vec{E}}}=\lambda\pmatrix{\cos(\omega t)&-\sin(\omega t)&0\cr\sin(\omega t)&\cos(\omega t)&0\cr 0&0&1}=
the expected rotation matrix expanded by
\lambda
(change of unit of measurement).

3.3.

Calculation of
{\Theta_{t}}
(affine):
{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S})={\Theta_{t}}(\vec{0})+d{\Theta_{t}}.\vec{y}_{\!S}
,
so, with
{O_{\!At}}={O_{\!Bt}}
here,

\vec{y}_{\!Dt}:={\Theta_{t}}(\vec{y}_{\!S})=d{\Theta_{t}}.\vec{y}_{\!S}

(10.67)

4.

Motions of
{\it O\!b\!j}
.

4.1.

B quantifies the motion
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
of
{\it O\!b\!j}
, i.e. he stores the relative motion
{\vec{\varphi}_{\!B}}
of
{\it O\!b\!j}
, and the relative velocities and accelerations
{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}
and
{{\vec{\gamma}}_{B}}
(matrices), cf. (
10.10
)-(
10.12
).

4.2.

Translations for A: With
\vec{x}_{\!At}={\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})
,

{{\vec{v}}_{Bt*}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})=d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})\quad\hbox{and}\quad{{\vec{\gamma}}_{Bt*}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})=d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{{\vec{\gamma}}_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).

(10.68)

5.

Drive force
(apparent force in
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}
due to the motion of B):

{\vec{f}}_{\!B\!Dt}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})=-m\,d{\Theta_{t}}^{-1}.{{\vec{\gamma}}_{Dt}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdaE})}}\lambda m\omega^{2}d{\Theta_{t}}^{-1}.\pmatrix{x_{A1}(t)\cr x_{A2}(t)\cr 0}\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdaE0})}}\lambda m\omega^{2}\pmatrix{x_{B1}(t)\cr x_{B2}(t)\cr 0},

(10.69)

centrifugal force (in a “parallel plane” at latitude of
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
).

6.

Coriolis acceleration
(apparent acceleration due to the motion of B):

{{\vec{\gamma}}_{Ct}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})=2\,d{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}}.(d{\Theta_{t}}.{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}))=2\,d{\Theta_{t}}.d{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}}.{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})

(10.70)

because
d{\Theta_{t}}
commutes with
d{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}}
(composition of “rotations along the same south-north axis”
which reads as
e^{i\omega t}.e^{i{\pi\over 2}}=e^{i{\pi\over 2}}e^{i\omega t}=e^{i({\pi\over 2}+\omega t)}
in the equatorial plane).

7.

Coriolis force
(apparent force due to the motion of B):

{\vec{f}}_{BCt}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})=-m\,d{\Theta_{t}}^{-1}.{{\vec{\gamma}}_{Ct}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})=-2m\,d{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}}.{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})=-2m{\vec{\omega}}\wedge{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).

(10.71)

11
Objectivities

Goal: To give an objective expression of the laws of mechanics; As Maxwell
[
15
]
said:
“The formula at which we arrive must be such that a person of any nation, by substituting for the different symbols the numerical value of the quantities as measured by his own national units, would arrive at a true result”.

Generic notation: if a function
z
is given as
z(t,x)
, then
z_{t}(x):=z(t,x)
, and conversely.

11.1
“Isometric objectivity” and “Frame Invariance Principle”

This manuscript is not intended to describe “isometric objectivity”:

“Isometric objectivity” is the framework in which the “principle of material frame-indifference”
(“frame invariance principle”) is settled, principle which states that
“Rigid body motions should not affect the stress constitutive law of a material”.
E.g., Truesdell–Noll
[
21
]
p. 41:

<< Constitutive equations must be invariant under changes of frame of reference. >>

Or Germain
[
11
]
:

<<
Axiom of power of internal forces.
The virtual power of the "internal forces" acting on a system S for a given virtual motion is an objective quantity; i.e., it has the same value whatever be the frame in which the motion is observed. >>

NB:
Both of these affirmations are limited to “isometric changes of frame”
(the same metric for all), as Truesdell–Noll
[
21
]

page 42-43
explain:
The “isometric objectivity”
concern one observer who defines his Euclidean dot product
and consider only orthonormal change of bases to validate a constitutive law.

If you want to interpret “isometric objectivity” in the “covariant objectivity” framework,
then “isometric objectivity” corresponds to a dictatorial management:
One observer with his Euclidean referential (e.g. based on the English foot),
imposes his unit of length to all other users (isometry hypothesis).
(Note: The metre was not adopted by the scientific community until after 1875.)

Moreover, isometric objectivity leads to despise the difference between covariance and contravariance,
due to the uncontrolled use of the Riesz representation theorem.

Remark 11.1

Marsden and Hughes
[
14
]
p. 8 use this isometric framework to begin with.
But, pages 22 and 163, they write that a “good modelization” has to be “covariant objective” (observer independent) to begin with;
And they propose a covariant modelization for elasticity at § 3.3.

11.2
Definition and characterization of the covariant objectivity

11.2.1
Framework of classical mechanics

Framework of classical mechanics to simplify.
Consider two observers A and B and their referentials
{{\cal R}_{\!A}}=({O_{\!A}},({\vec{A}}_{i}))
and
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}=({O_{\!B}},({\vec{B}}_{i}))
.
E.g.,
({\vec{A}}_{i})
and
({\vec{B}}_{i})
are Euclidean bases in foot and metre,
(\cdot,\cdot)_{A}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{B}
is their associated Euclidean dot products.
And
\Theta
is the translator, cf. (
10.19
).

Consider a regular motion
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
of an object
{\it O\!b\!j}
,
{p_{t}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
the position at
t
of a particle in our Universe,
{\Omega_{t}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{\it O\!b\!j})
the configuration at
t
, and
\hbox{\large$\cal C$}=\bigcup_{t\in[a,b]}(\{t\}\times{\Omega_{t}})
the set of configurations.
And
\vec{x}_{\!At}:=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}{p_{t}}}]_{|{\vec{A}}}\in{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{A}\!)
and
\vec{x}_{\!Bt}:=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}{p_{t}}}]_{|{\vec{B}}}\in{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{B}\!)
are the stored components
of
{p_{t}}
relative to the chosen referentials,
{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{A}\!)
and
{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{B}\!)
being the spaces of
n*1
matrices as referred to by
A
and
B
.

11.2.2
Covariant objectivity of a scalar function

Let
f:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\hbox{\large$\cal C$}&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr(t,{p_{t}})&\rightarrow f(t,{p_{t}})\cr}}\,\right\}
be a Eulerian scalar function (e.g., a temperature field).
f
is quantified by A and B as the functions
{f_{A}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\mathbb{R}{\times}{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{A}\!)&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr(t,\vec{x}_{\!At})&\rightarrow{f_{A}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!At}):=f(t,{p_{t}})\cr}}\,\right\}

and

{f_{B}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\mathbb{R}{\times}{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{B}\!)&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt})&\rightarrow{f_{B}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt}):=f(t,{p_{t}})\cr}}\,\right\}
.

Definition 11.2

f
is objective covariant iff, for all referentials
{{\cal R}_{\!A}}
and
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}
and for all
t
,

{f_{At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})={f_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})\quad\hbox{when}\quad\vec{x}_{\!At}={\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}),

(11.1)

i.e.
{f_{At}}=f_{Bt*}
is the push-forward of
{f_{Bt}}
by
{\Theta_{t}}
cf. (
6.8
).

11.2.3
Covariant objectivity of a vector field

Let
{\vec{w}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\hbox{\large$\cal C$}&\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\cr(t,{p_{t}})&\rightarrow{\vec{w}}(t,{p_{t}})\cr}}\,\right\}
be a Eulerian vector field (e.g., a force field).

{\vec{w}}
is quantified by A and B as the functions

{{\vec{w}}_{\!A}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\mathbb{R}{\times}{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{A}\!)&\rightarrow{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{A}\!)\cr(t,\vec{x}_{\!At})&\rightarrow{{\vec{w}}_{\!A}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!At}):=[{\vec{w}}(t,{p_{t}})]_{{\vec{A}}}\cr}}\,\right\}

and

{{\vec{w}}_{B}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\mathbb{R}{\times}{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{B}\!)&\rightarrow{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{B}\!)\cr(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt})&\rightarrow{{\vec{w}}_{B}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt}):=[{\vec{w}}(t,{p_{t}})]_{{\vec{B}}}\cr}}\,\right\}
.
So
{{\vec{w}}_{\!A}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!At})
and

{{\vec{w}}_{B}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt})
are the column matrices of the components of
{\vec{w}}(t,{p_{t}})
in
{{\cal R}_{\!A}}
and
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}
.

Definition 11.3

{\vec{w}}
is objective covariant iff,
for all referentials
{{\cal R}_{\!A}}
and
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}
and for all
t
,

{{\vec{w}}_{\!At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})=d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{{\vec{w}}_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})\quad\hbox{when}\quad\vec{x}_{\!At}={\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}),

(11.2)

i.e.
{{\vec{w}}_{\!At}}={{\vec{w}}_{Bt*}}
is the push-forward of
{{\vec{w}}_{Bt}}
by
{\Theta_{t}}
cf. (
6.20
).

Example 11.4

Fundamental counter-example: A Eulerian velocity field is not objective, cf. (
10.39
),
because of the drive velocity
{\vec{v}}_{D}\neq\vec{0}
in general.
Neither is a Eulerian acceleration field, cf. (
10.45
).

Example 11.5

The field of gravitational forces (external forces) is objective covariant.

11.2.4
Covariant objectivity of differential forms

Let
\alpha:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\hbox{\large$\cal C$}&\rightarrow{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{*}}\cr(t,{p_{t}})&\rightarrow\alpha(t,{p_{t}})\cr}}\,\right\}

be a Eulerian differential form (e.g. a measuring device used to get the internal power).

\alpha
is quantified by A and B as the functions

{\alpha_{A}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\mathbb{R}{\times}{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{A}\!)&\rightarrow{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{A}\!)\cr(t,\vec{x}_{\!At})&\rightarrow{\alpha_{A}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!At}):=[\alpha(t,{p_{t}})]_{{\vec{A}}}\cr}}\,\right\}

and

{\alpha_{B}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\mathbb{R}{\times}{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{B}\!)&\rightarrow{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}\!(\!\hbox{B}\!)\cr(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt})&\rightarrow{\alpha_{B}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt}):=[\alpha(t,{p_{t}})]_{{\vec{B}}}\cr}}\,\right\}
.
So
{\alpha_{A}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!At})
and

{\alpha_{B}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt})
are the row matrices of the components of
\alpha(t,{p_{t}})
in
{{\cal R}_{\!A}}
and
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}
.

Definition 11.6

\alpha
is objective covariant iff,
for all referentials
{{\cal R}_{\!A}}
and
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}
and for all
t
,

{\alpha_{At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})={\alpha_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})^{-1}\quad\hbox{when}\quad\vec{x}_{\!At}={\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).

(11.3)

i.e.
{\alpha_{At}}=\alpha_{Bt*}
is the push-forward of
{\alpha_{Bt}}
by
{\Theta_{t}}
cf. (
7.3
).

NB: (
11.3
) and (
11.2
) are compatible:
If
{\vec{w}}
is an objective vector field and if
\alpha
is an objective differential form,
then the scalar function
\alpha.{\vec{w}}
is objective:

{\alpha_{At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At}).{{\vec{w}}_{\!At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})={\alpha_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{{\vec{w}}_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})\quad(=(\alpha(t,{p_{t}}).{\vec{w}}(t,{p_{t}})),

(11.4)

since

{\alpha_{At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At}).{{\vec{w}}_{\!At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})=({\alpha_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})^{-1}).(d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{{\vec{w}}_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}))={\alpha_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{{\vec{w}}_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})
.

11.2.5
Covariant objectivity of tensors

A tensor acts on both vector fields and differential forms,
and its objectivity is deduced from the previous §.

So, let
T
be a (Eulerian) tensor corresponding to a “physical quantity”.
The observers
A
and
B
describe
T
as being the functions

T_{A}
and
T_{B}
.

Definition 11.7

T
is objective covariant iff,
for all referentials
{{\cal R}_{\!A}}
and
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}
and for all
t
,

T_{At}(\vec{x}_{\!At})=T_{Bt*}(\vec{x}_{\!At})

(11.5)

i.e.
T_{At}
is the push-forward of
T_{Bt}
by
{\Theta_{t}}
.

(Recall:

T_{Bt*}(\vec{x}_{\!At})(\alpha_{1}(\vec{x}_{\!At}),...,{\vec{w}}_{1}(\vec{x}_{\!At})):=T_{Bt}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})(\alpha_{1}{}^{*}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}),...,{\vec{w}}_{1}{}^{*}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}))
.)

Example 11.8
(Non covariant objectivity of a differential
d{\vec{w}}
)

Let
{\vec{w}}
be an objective vector field, seen as
{{\vec{w}}_{\!A}}
by A and
{{\vec{w}}_{B}}
by B;
So
{{\vec{w}}_{\!At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdefocv})}d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{{\vec{w}}_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})
when
\vec{x}_{\!At}={\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})
,
thus

d{{\vec{w}}_{\!At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At}).d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})=d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).d{{\vec{w}}_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})+(d^{2}{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{{\vec{w}}_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})),

(11.6)

hence

\displaystyle{d{{\vec{w}}_{\!At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})=}

\displaystyle{{}d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).d{{\vec{w}}_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})^{-1}+(d^{2}{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{{\vec{w}}_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})).d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})^{-1}}

\displaystyle{\neq}

\displaystyle{{}d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).d{{\vec{w}}_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})^{-1}\quad\hbox{when}\quad d^{2}{\Theta_{t}}\neq 0.}

(11.7)

Thus
d{\vec{w}}
is not covariant objective in general. However in classical mechanics for “change of Cartesian referentials”
{\Theta_{t}}
is affine, so
d^{2}{\Theta_{t}}=0
, and in particular
d{\vec{w}}
is objective when
{\vec{w}}
is.
And

\displaystyle{{}(d^{2}{{\vec{w}}_{\!At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At}).d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})).d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})+d{{\vec{w}}_{\!At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At}).d^{2}{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})}

\displaystyle{{}=d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).d^{2}{{\vec{w}}_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})+2\,d^{2}{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).d{{\vec{w}}_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})+d^{3}{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{{\vec{w}}_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).}

(11.8)

Thus
d^{2}{\vec{w}}
