is not covariant objective in general (but if
{\Theta_{t}}
is affine then
d^{2}{\vec{w}}
is objective if
{\vec{w}}
is).

11.3
Non objectivity of the velocities

11.3.1
Eulerian velocity
{\vec{v}}\,
: not covariant (and not isometric) objective

Velocity addition formala: With
{{\vec{v}}_{Bt*}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})=d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{\vec{w}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})
when
\vec{x}_{\!At}={\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})
, cf. (
10.39
),

\displaystyle{{{\vec{v}}_{\!At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})=}

\displaystyle{{}{{\vec{v}}_{Bt*}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})+{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})}

\displaystyle{\neq}

\displaystyle{{}{{\vec{v}}_{Bt*}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})\quad\hbox{when}\quad{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})\neq\vec{0},}

(11.9)

thus a Eulerian velocity field is not covariant objective (and not isometric objective).

11.3.2
d{\vec{v}}
is not objective

The velocity addition formula,
({{\vec{v}}_{\!At}}-{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}})(\vec{x}_{\!At})={{\vec{v}}_{Bt*}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})=d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})
when
\vec{x}_{\!At}={\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})
, gives

d({{\vec{v}}_{\!At}}-{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}})(\vec{x}_{\!At}).d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})=d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).d{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})+d^{2}{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}),

(11.10)

thus
d{\vec{v}}
is neither covariant objective nor isometric objective because of
d{\vec{v}_{\!D}}
:

d{{\vec{v}}_{\!At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})=d{{\vec{v}}_{Bt*}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})+d{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})+d^{2}{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})^{-1}\neq d{{\vec{v}}_{Bt*}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})\quad\hbox{in general}.

(11.11)

Remark 11.9

Recall: “Isometric objective” implies

\bullet
The use of the same Euclidean metric in
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}
and
{{\cal R}_{\!A}}
, i.e.
(\cdot,\cdot)_{A}=(\cdot,\cdot)_{B}
,

\bullet

{{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}
(motion of
{{\cal R}_{\!B}}
) is a solid body motion, and

\bullet

{\Theta_{t}}
is affine (so
d^{2}{\Theta_{t}}=0
for all
t
).

Exercice 11.10

Prove, with
Q_{t}
the (orthonormal) transition matrix from
({\vec{A}}_{i})
to
({\vec{B}}_{i})
:

[d{\vec{v}}_{t}]_{|{\vec{B}}}=Q_{t}.[d{\vec{v}}_{t}]_{|{\vec{A}}}.Q_{t}^{-1}+Q^{\prime}(t).Q_{t}^{-1},\quad\hbox{written}\quad[L]_{|{\vec{B}}}=Q.[L]_{|{\vec{A}}}.Q^{T}+\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptQ}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}.Q^{T}.

(11.12)

(Used in classical mechanics courses, to prove that
d{\vec{v}}
isn’t “isometric objective” because of
\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptQ}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}.Q^{T}
.)

Answer
.
{t_{0}},t\in\mathbb{R}
,

{p_{t_{0}}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}({t_{0}},{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
,
{p_{t}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})
,
{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}})={\partial{\widetilde{\Phi}}\over\partial t}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
,
and
{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})
.
So
{\vec{v}}(t,{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}))={\partial{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}\over\partial t}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})
,
thus
d{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}}).{F^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t)={\partial{F^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}\over\partial t}(t)
.
And (
4.30
), with
{F^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}F
, gives
[F(t)]_{|{\vec{a}}_{t_{0}},{\vec{B}}}=Q(t).[F(t)]_{|{\vec{a}}_{t_{0}},{\vec{A}}}
,
thus
[F^{\prime}(t)]_{|{\vec{a}}_{t_{0}},{\vec{B}}}=Q^{\prime}(t).[F(t)]_{|{\vec{a}}_{t_{0}},{\vec{A}}}+Q(t).[F^{\prime}(t)]_{|{\vec{a}}_{t_{0}},{\vec{A}}}
.
Thus
[d{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}})]_{|{\vec{B}}}=[{F^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}^{\prime}(t).{F^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t)]_{|{\vec{B}}}=[{F^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}^{\prime}(t)]_{|{\vec{B}}}.[{F^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t)]_{|{\vec{B}}}=(Q^{\prime}(t).[F(t)]_{|{\vec{a}}_{t_{0}},{\vec{A}}}+Q(t).[F^{\prime}(t)]_{|{\vec{a}}_{t_{0}},{\vec{A}}}).[F(t)]_{|{\vec{a}}_{t_{0}},{\vec{A}}}^{-1}.Q(t)^{-1}=Q^{\prime}(t).Q(t)^{-1}+Q(t).[F^{\prime}(t)]_{|{\vec{a}}_{t_{0}},{\vec{A}}}.[F(t)]_{|{\vec{a}}_{t_{0}},{\vec{A}}}^{-1}.Q(t)^{-1}=Q^{\prime}(t).Q(t)^{-1}+Q(t).[d{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}})]_{|{\vec{A}}}.Q(t)^{-1}
. And cf. (
3.34
)-(
3.35
).

Exercice 11.11

Prove that
d^{2}{\vec{v}}
is “isometric objective” when
{{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}
is a rigid body motion.

Answer
. (
11.8
) with
{{\vec{v}}_{\!A}}-{\vec{v}}_{D}
instead of
{\vec{w}}_{A}
, and
{\vec{v}}_{B}
instead of
{\vec{w}}_{B}

give, in an “isometric objective” framework,

d^{2}({{\vec{v}}_{\!At}}-{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}})(\vec{x}_{\!At}).({{\vec{u}}_{Bt*}},{{\vec{w}}_{Bt*}})=d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).d^{2}{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})({{\vec{u}}_{B}},{{\vec{w}}_{B}}).

(11.13)

Here
d^{2}{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}}=0
(rigid body motion), thus
d^{2}{\vec{v}}
is “isometric objective”.

11.3.3
d{\vec{v}}+d{\vec{v}}^{T}
is “isometric objective”

Proposition 11.12

If
{{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}
is a rigid body motion then
d{\vec{v}}_{t}+d{\vec{v}}_{t}^{T}
is “isometric objective”

d{{\vec{v}}_{\!At}}+d{{\vec{v}}_{\!At}}^{T}=(d{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}+d{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}^{T})_{*}.

(11.14)

(Isometric framework: The rate of deformation tensor is independent of an added added rigid motion.)

Proof.
Q.Q^{T}=I
gives
\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptQ}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}.Q^{T}+(\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptQ}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}.Q^{T})^{T}=0
, then apply (
11.12
).

Exercice 11.13

Prove that
\Omega={d{\vec{v}}-d{\vec{v}}^{T}\over 2}
is not isometric objective.

Answer
. (
11.11
) gives
d{{\vec{v}}_{\!At}}^{T}=d{{\vec{v}}_{Bt*}}^{T}+d{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}}^{T}
, thus

{d{{\vec{v}}_{\!At}}-d{{\vec{v}}_{\!At}}^{T}\over 2}={d{{\vec{v}}_{Bt*}}-d{{\vec{v}}_{Bt*}}^{T}\over 2}+{d{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}}-d{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}}^{T}\over 2}\neq{d{{\vec{v}}_{Bt*}}-d{{\vec{v}}_{Bt*}}^{T}\over 2}
,
even if
{{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{\!{\cal R}_{\!\!B}}}
is a solid body motion (then
{d{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}}-d{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}}^{T}\over 2}={\vec{\omega}}\wedge
is a rotation time a dilation).

11.3.4
Lagrangian velocities

The Lagrangian velocities do not define a vector field, cf. §
3.2.2
.
Thus asking about the objectivity of Lagrangian velocities is meaningless.

11.4
The Lie derivatives are covariant objective

Framework of §
10
. In particular we have the velocity-addition formula
{{\vec{v}}_{\!At}}={{\vec{v}}_{Bt*}}+{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}}
in
{{\cal R}_{\!A}}
where
{{\vec{v}}_{Bt*}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})=d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})
and
\vec{x}_{\!Bt}={\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})
, cf. (
10.39
).

The objectivity under concern is the covariant objectivity
(no inner dot product or basis required).
The Lie derivatives are also called “objective rates” because they are covariant objectives.
Easy proofs.

11.4.1
Scalar functions

Proposition 11.14

If
f
be a covariant objective function, cf. (
11.1
),
then its Lie derivative
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}f
is covariant objective:

{\cal L}_{{{\vec{v}}_{\!A}}}{f_{A}}=\Theta_{*}({\cal L}_{{{\vec{v}}_{\!B}}}{f_{B}}),\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad{\cal L}_{{{\vec{v}}_{\!A}}}{f_{A}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!At})={\cal L}_{{{\vec{v}}_{\!B}}}{f_{B}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt})\quad\hbox{when}\quad\vec{x}_{\!At}={\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}),

(11.15)

i.e.,
{D{f_{A}}\over Dt}(t,\vec{x}_{\!At})={D{f_{B}}\over Dt}(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt})
, i.e.
({\partial{f_{A}}\over\partial t}+d{f_{A}}.{{\vec{v}}_{\!A}})(t,\vec{x}_{\!At})=({\partial{f_{B}}\over\partial t}+d{f_{B}}.{{\vec{v}}_{\!B}})(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt})
.

Proof.
Consider the motion
t\rightarrow p(t)={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
of a particle
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
,
and
\vec{x}_{\!A}(t)=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!A}}p(t)}]_{|{\vec{A}}}
and
\vec{x}_{\!B}(t)=[\overrightarrow{{O_{\!B}}p(t)}]_{|{\vec{B}}}
.
With
f
objective, (
11.1
) gives
{f_{B}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!B}(t))={f_{A}}(t,\Theta(t,\vec{x}_{\!B}(t)))
(
={f_{A}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!A}(t))
),
thus

\displaystyle{{D{f_{B}}\over Dt}(t,\vec{x}_{\!B}(t))=}

\displaystyle{{}{\partial{f_{A}}\over\partial t}(t,\vec{x}_{\!A}(t))+d{f_{At}}(\vec{x}_{\!A}(t)).(\underbrace{{\partial\Theta\over\partial t}(t,\vec{x}_{\!B}(t))}_{{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})}+\underbrace{d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!B}(t)).{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!B}(t)))}_{{{\vec{v}}_{Bt*}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}{\partial{f_{A}}\over\partial t}(t,\vec{x}_{\!At})+d{f_{At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At}).{{\vec{v}}_{\!At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})={D{f_{A}}\over Dt}(t,\vec{x}_{\!At}),}

(11.16)

thanks to velocity addiction formula
{{\vec{v}}_{\!At}}={{\vec{v}}_{Bt*}}+{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}}
.

11.4.2
Vector fields

Proposition 11.15

Let
{\vec{w}}
be a covariant objective vector field, cf. (
11.2
).
Then its Lie derivative
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}
is covariant objective:

{\cal L}_{{{\vec{v}}_{\!A}}}{\vec{w}}_{A}=\Theta_{*}({\cal L}_{{{\vec{v}}_{\!B}}}{{\vec{w}}_{B}}),

(11.17)

i.e., when
\vec{x}_{\!At}={\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})
,

{\cal L}_{{{\vec{v}}_{\!A}}}{\vec{w}}_{A}(t,\vec{x}_{\!At})=d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{\cal L}_{{{\vec{v}}_{\!B}}}{{\vec{w}}_{B}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt}),

(11.18)

i.e.,

({D{\vec{w}}_{A}\over Dt}-d{{\vec{v}}_{\!A}}.{\vec{w}}_{A})(t,\vec{x}_{\!At})=d\Theta(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).({D{{\vec{w}}_{B}}\over Dt}-d{{\vec{v}}_{\!B}}.{{\vec{w}}_{B}})(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt}),

(11.19)

i.e.,

({\partial{\vec{w}}_{A}\over\partial t}+d{\vec{w}}_{A}.{{\vec{v}}_{\!A}}-d{{\vec{v}}_{\!A}}.{\vec{w}}_{A})(t,\vec{x}_{\!At})=d\Theta(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).({\partial{{\vec{w}}_{B}}\over\partial t}+d{{\vec{w}}_{B}}.{{\vec{v}}_{\!B}}-d{{\vec{v}}_{\!B}}.{{\vec{w}}_{B}})(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).

(11.20)

But the partial, convected, material, and Lie autonomous derivatives are not covariant objective
(not even isometric objective because of the drive velocity
{\vec{v}_{\!D}}
): We have

(d{{\vec{w}}_{\!At}}.({{\vec{v}}_{\!At}}{-}{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}}))(\vec{x}_{\!At})=(d{\Theta_{t}}.(d{{\vec{w}}_{Bt}}.{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}})+(d^{2}{\Theta_{t}}.{{\vec{w}}_{Bt}}).{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}})(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}),

(11.21)

(d({{\vec{v}}_{\!At}}{-}{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}}).{{\vec{w}}_{\!At}})(\vec{x}_{\!At})=(d{\Theta_{t}}.(d{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}.{{\vec{w}}_{Bt}})+(d^{2}{\Theta_{t}}.{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}).{{\vec{w}}_{Bt}})(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}),

(11.22)

(d({{\vec{v}}_{\!At}}{-}{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}}).({{\vec{v}}_{\!At}}{-}{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}}))(\vec{x}_{\!At})=(d{\Theta_{t}}.(d{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}.{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}})+d^{2}{\Theta_{t}}({{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}},{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}))(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}),

(11.23)

{\cal L}^{0}_{({{\vec{v}}_{\!At}}-{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}})}{{\vec{w}}_{\!At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})=d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{\cal L}^{0}_{{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}}{{\vec{w}}_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}),

(11.24)

{\partial{{\vec{w}}_{\!A}}\over\partial t}(t,\vec{x}_{\!At})+{\cal L}^{0}_{{\vec{v}}_{D}}{{\vec{w}}_{\!At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})=d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{\partial{{\vec{w}}_{B}}\over\partial t}(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt}),

(11.25)

\displaystyle{{D{{\vec{w}}_{\!A}}\over Dt}(t,\vec{x}_{\!At})-d{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}}.{{\vec{w}}_{\!At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})=d{\Theta_{t}}.(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{D{{\vec{w}}_{B}}\over Dt}(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt})+d^{2}{\Theta_{t}}({{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}},{{\vec{w}}_{Bt}})(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}),}

(11.26)

\displaystyle{{\partial({{\vec{v}}_{\!A}}{-}{\vec{v}}_{D})\over\partial t}(t,\vec{x}_{\!At})+{\cal L}^{0}_{{\vec{v}}_{D}}({{\vec{v}}_{\!A}}{-}{\vec{v}}_{D})(t,\vec{x}_{\!At})=}

\displaystyle{{}d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{\partial{{\vec{v}}_{\!B}}\over\partial t}(t,\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).}

(11.27)

Proof.
\bullet

{{\vec{w}}_{\!At}}({\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}))=d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{{\vec{w}}_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})
gives

d{{\vec{w}}_{\!At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At}).d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})=d^{2}{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{{\vec{w}}_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})+d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).d{{\vec{w}}_{B}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}),

(11.28)

thus, with
d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})=({{\vec{v}}_{\!At}}{-}{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}})(\vec{x}_{\!At})={{\vec{v}}_{Bt*}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})
(velocity-addition formula),

d{{\vec{w}}_{\!At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At}).({{\vec{v}}_{\!At}}{-}{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}})(\vec{x}_{\!At})=(d^{2}{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})).{{\vec{w}}_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})+d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).d{{\vec{w}}_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}),

hence (
11.21
). In particular

d{{\vec{w}}_{\!At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At}).{{\vec{v}}_{\!At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})\neq d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).(d{{\vec{w}}_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}))

(the vector field
d{\vec{w}}.{\vec{v}}
is not objective).

\bullet

({{\vec{v}}_{\!At}}{-}{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}})({\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}))=d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})
gives

d({{\vec{v}}_{\!At}}{-}{{\vec{v}}_{\!Dt}})(\vec{x}_{\!At}).d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})=d^{2}{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt})+d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}).d{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!Bt}),

so, applied to
{{\vec{w}}_{Bt}}
(resp.
{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}
), we get (
11.22
) (resp. (
11.23
)).
Hence (
11.24
).

\bullet

If
\vec{x}_{\!At}={\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!B})
, then
{\vec{w}}_{A}(t,\Theta(t,\vec{x}_{\!B}))=d\Theta(t,\vec{x}_{\!B}).{{\vec{w}}_{B}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!B})
, so,
with
{\partial\Theta\over\partial t}(t,\vec{x}_{\!B})={{\vec{v}}_{\Theta t}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})
, we get

\displaystyle{{\partial{\vec{w}}_{A}\over\partial t}(t,\vec{x}_{\!At})+d{{\vec{w}}_{\!At}}(\vec{x}_{\!At}).{{\vec{v}}_{\Theta t}}(\vec{x}_{\!At})=}

\displaystyle{{}d{\partial\Theta\over\partial t}(t,\vec{x}_{\!B}).{{\vec{w}}_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!B})+d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!B}).{\partial{{\vec{w}}_{B}}\over\partial t}(t,\vec{x}_{\!B})}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}(d{{\vec{v}}_{\Theta t}}(\vec{x}_{\!At}).d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!B})).{{\vec{w}}_{Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!B})+d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!B}).{\partial{{\vec{w}}_{B}}\over\partial t}(t,\vec{x}_{\!B}),}

Thus (
11.25
) since
{{\vec{v}}_{\Theta}}={\vec{v}}_{D}
;
Then (
11.21
) gives (
11.26
).

\bullet

{\vec{v}}_{B*}(t,\Theta(t,\vec{x}_{\!B}))=d\Theta(t,\vec{x}_{\!B}).{{\vec{v}}_{\!B}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!B})
gives

\displaystyle{{\partial{\vec{v}}_{B*}\over\partial t}(t,\vec{x}_{\!At})+d{\vec{v}}_{B*}(\vec{x}_{\!At}).{{\vec{v}}_{\Theta}}(t,\vec{x}_{\!At})=}

\displaystyle{{}\underbrace{{\partial d\Theta\over\partial t}(t,\vec{x}_{\!B})}_{d{{\vec{v}}_{\Theta t}}(\vec{x}_{\!At}).d{\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!B})}.{{\vec{v}}_{\!Bt}}(\vec{x}_{\!B})+d\Theta(t,\vec{x}_{\!B}).{\partial{{\vec{v}}_{\!B}}\over\partial t}(t,\vec{x}_{\!B},)}

since
{\partial d\Theta\over\partial t}(t,\vec{x}_{\!B})=d({\partial\Theta\over\partial t})(t,\vec{x}_{\!B})
and

{\partial\Theta\over\partial t}(t,\vec{x}_{\!B})={\vec{v}}_{\Theta}(t,\vec{x}_{\!At})={\vec{v}}_{\Theta_{t}}({\Theta_{t}}(\vec{x}_{\!B}))
;
hence (
11.27
).

11.4.3
Tensors

Proposition 11.16

It
T
is a covariant objective tensor, then its Lie derivatives are covariant objectives:

{\cal L}_{{{\vec{v}}_{\!A}}}T_{A}=\Theta_{*}({\cal L}_{{{\vec{v}}_{\!B}}}T_{B}).

(11.29)

Proof.
Corollary of (
11.15
) and (
11.18
)
to get
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}(\alpha.{\vec{w}})=({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}\alpha).{\vec{w}}+\alpha.({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}})
;
Then use
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}(t_{1}\otimes t_{2})=({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}t_{1})\otimes t_{2}+t_{1}\otimes({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}t_{2})
.

11.5
Taylor expansions and ubiquity gift

11.5.1
In
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
with ubiquity

Generic formula:

f(t)=f({t_{0}})+(t{-}{t_{0}})\,f^{\prime}({t_{0}})+{(t{-}{t_{0}})^{2}\over 2}\,f^{\prime}({t_{0}})^{2}+o((t{-}{t_{0}})^{2}).

(11.30)

In particular
f(t)={\vec{w}}(t,p(t))
gives

\displaystyle{{\vec{w}}(t,p(t))=}

\displaystyle{{}{\vec{w}}({t_{0}},p({t_{0}}))+(t{-}{t_{0}})\,{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}({t_{0}},p({t_{0}}))+{(t{-}{t_{0}})^{2}\over 2}\,{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}({t_{0}},p({t_{0}}))^{2}+o((t{-}{t_{0}})^{2}).}

(11.31)

Problem :

{\vec{w}}(t,p(t))
is a vecteur at
t
at
p(t)

while
{\vec{w}}({t_{0}},p({t_{0}}))
is a vecteur at
{t_{0}}
at
p({t_{0}})
,
so (
11.31
) cannot be written

{\vec{w}}(t,p(t))-\bigl({\vec{w}}({t_{0}},p({t_{0}}))+(t{-}{t_{0}})\,{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}({t_{0}},p({t_{0}}))+{(t{-}{t_{0}})^{2}\over 2}\,{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}({t_{0}},p({t_{0}}))^{2}\bigr)=o((t{-}{t_{0}})^{2}),

(11.32)

since the left-hand side supposes the ubiquity gift.

E.g. in a non-planar manifold (e.g. a surface in
{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
considered on its own),

{\vec{w}}(t,{p_{t}})\in T_{\!{p_{t}}}\!({\Omega_{t}})=
the linear tangent space at
p(t)={p_{t}}
,
whereas
{\vec{w}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})\in T_{\!{p_{t_{0}}}}\!({\Omega_{t_{0}}})=
the linear tangent space at
p({t_{0}})={p_{t_{0}}}
,
and the tangent spaces
T_{\!{p_{t}}}\!({\Omega_{t}})
and
T_{\!{p_{t_{0}}}}\!({\Omega_{t_{0}}})
are distinct at two distinct points in general;
Thus the left-hand side of (
11.32
) is meaningless.

In
{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
our affine space (our Universe),
T_{\!{p_{t}}}\!({\Omega_{t}})
and
T_{\!{p_{t_{0}}}}\!({\Omega_{t_{0}}})
are identified with
{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}
,
and (
11.31
) is well defined, and very useful!

11.5.2
General case

By definition, cf. (
9.10
), with
p(t)={\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})
,

{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})={d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})^{-1}.{\vec{w}}(t,p(t))-{\vec{w}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})\over t-{t_{0}}}+o(1).

(11.33)

Thus,

d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})^{-1}.{\vec{w}}(t,p(t))={\vec{w}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})+(t{-}{t_{0}})\,{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})+o(t{-}{t_{0}}).

(11.34)

Hence we get the first order Taylor expansion without ubiquity gift:

{\vec{w}}(t,p(t))=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).\Bigl({\vec{w}}+(t{-}{t_{0}})\,{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}\Bigr)({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})+o(t{-}{t_{0}}),

(11.35)

both side of the equality being in
T_{\!{p_{t}}}\!({\Omega_{t}})
(meaningful in any manifold).

Proposition 11.17

In
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
, with the gift of ubiquity, (
11.35
) gives (
11.31
).

Proof.
d{\Phi^{t_{0}}}({t_{0}}{+}h,{p_{t_{0}}})).{\vec{w}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdPhi2b})}}{\vec{w}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})+h\,d{\vec{v}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{w}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})+o(h)
, thus

\displaystyle{{\vec{w}}(t,{p_{t}})\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdlpb20})}}}

\displaystyle{{}(I+h\,d{\vec{v}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})+o(h)).\Bigl(({\vec{w}}+h\,{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}-h\,d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}})({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})+o(h)\Bigr)}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}({\vec{w}}+h\,d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}+h\,{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}-h\,d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}})({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})+o(h)=({\vec{w}}+h\,{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt})({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})+o(h),}

which is (
11.31
).

Proposition 11.18

In
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
, at second order,

{\vec{w}}(t,p(t))=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).\bigl(({\vec{w}}+h{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}+{h^{2}\over 2}{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}))({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})+o(h^{2})\bigr).

(11.36)

So if the values
{\vec{w}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})
,
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})
and
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}})({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})

are known, then
{\vec{w}}(t,p(t))
is estimated at second order thanks to the push-forward of

({\vec{w}}+h{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}+{h^{2}\over 2}{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}))({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})
by
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
.

Proof.
Let
d{\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})={F^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t)
. Let
{\vec{g}}(t)=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})^{-1}.{\vec{w}}(t,p(t))
when
p(t)={\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})
.
So
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})={{\vec{g}}\,^{\prime}}({t_{0}})
, cf. (
9.11
).
And

{D{\vec{w}}\over Du}(u,p(u))=d{\vec{v}}(u,p(u)).{\vec{w}}(u,p(u))+{F^{t}_{u}}({p_{t}}).{{\vec{g}}\,^{\prime}}(u)
, cf. (
9.16
).
Thus

{D^{2}{\vec{w}}\over Du^{2}}(u,p(u))={D(d{\vec{v}})\over Du}(u,p(u)).{\vec{w}}(u,p(u))+d{\vec{v}}(u,p(u)).{D{\vec{w}}\over Du}(u,p(u))+d{\vec{v}}(u,p(u).{F^{t}_{u}}({p_{t}}).{{\vec{g}}\,^{\prime}}(u)+{F^{t}_{u}}({p_{t}}).{{\vec{g}}\,^{\prime\prime}}(u)
.
Thus

{D^{2}{\vec{w}}\over Dt^{2}}(t,p(t))=({D(d{\vec{v}})\over Dt}.{\vec{w}}+d{\vec{v}}.{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}+d{\vec{v}}.{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}})(t,p(t))+{{\vec{g}}\,^{\prime\prime}}(t)
.
With (
9.39
) we get

{{\vec{g}}\,^{\prime\prime}}(t)={\cal L}_{\vec{v}}({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}})(t,p(t))
, cf. (
9.39
).

Alternate proof (calculation): (
4.34
) gives

{F^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t)=I_{t_{0}}+h\,d{\vec{v}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})+{h^{2}\over 2}\,d{\vec{\gamma}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})+o(h^{2})
.
Thus, omitting the reference to
({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})
to lighten the writing,

\displaystyle{{}d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).({\vec{w}}+h{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}+{h^{2}\over 2}{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}+o(h^{2}))}

\displaystyle{{}=\Bigl(I+h\,d{\vec{v}}+{h^{2}\over 2}\,d({D{\vec{v}}\over Dt})+o(h^{2})\Bigr).\Bigl({\vec{w}}+h{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}+{h^{2}\over 2}{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}+o(h^{2})\Bigr)}

(11.37)

The
h^{0}
term is
I.{\vec{w}}={\vec{w}}
.
The
h
term is
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}+d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}={D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}
.
The
h^{2}
term is the sum of

\displaystyle{\bullet}

\displaystyle{{}{1\over 2}{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}={1\over 2}({D^{2}{\vec{w}}\over Dt^{2}}-2\,d{\vec{v}}.{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}-{D(d{\vec{v}})\over Dt}.{\vec{w}}+d{\vec{v}}.d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}),\;\hbox{ cf{.}(\ref{eqcalvv})},}

\displaystyle{\bullet}

\displaystyle{{}d{\vec{v}}.{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}=d{\vec{v}}.{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}-d{\vec{v}}.d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}={1\over 2}(2d{\vec{v}}.{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}-2d{\vec{v}}.d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}),}

\displaystyle{\bullet}

\displaystyle{{}{1\over 2}d({D{\vec{v}}\over Dt}).{\vec{w}}={1\over 2}({D(d{\vec{v}})\over Dt}.{\vec{w}}+d{\vec{v}}.d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}),\;\hbox{ cf{.}(\ref{eqdfddtd2})}.}

And the sum gives
{D^{2}{\vec{w}}\over Dt^{2}}
.

Part V
Appendix

In this appendix, we tried to give standard results useful in mechanics, results that are scattered in the existing literature, and sometimes difficult to find except in math books (differential geometry).

The definitions, notations and results are detailed, so that no ambiguity is possible
(some notations can be nightmarish when not understood, or misused, or come like a bull in a china-shop).
All the results presented apply to solids, fluids, thermodynamics, general relativity, electromagnetism, quantum mechanics, chemistry… (the same math applies to all… even applies to mechanical engineers…).

Appendix A
Classical and duality notations

A.1
Contravariant vector and basis

A.1.1
Contravariant vector

Let
(E,+,.){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}E
be a real vector space (= a linear space on the field
\mathbb{R}
).

Definition A.1

An element
{\vec{x}}\in E
is called a vector, or a “contravariant vector”.

A vector is a vector…
So why this name contravariant?
Historical answer: Because of the change of basis formula

[{\vec{x}}]_{|{new}}=P^{-1}.[{\vec{x}}]_{|{old}}
, see (
A.28
), which uses
P^{-1}
.

So, what is a covariant vector? Answer: From the vector space
E
, you can build the vector space (an overlay)
{\cal L}(E;\mathbb{R}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}E^{*}=
the space of linear forms on
E
(a linear form is a measuring instrument that gives values to vectors).
Then an element
\ell\in E^{*}
will be called a covariant vector, because of the change of basis formula
[\ell]_{|{new}}=[\ell]_{|{old}}.P
. See §
A.5
for details.

A.1.2
Basis

Definitions:

\bullet

n
vectors
{\vec{e}}_{1},...,{\vec{e}}_{n}\in E
are linearly independent iff,
for all
\lambda,...,\lambda_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
, the equality
{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\lambda_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}=\vec{0}
implies

\lambda_{i}=0
for all
i=1,...,n
.

\bullet

n
vectors
{\vec{e}}_{1},...,{\vec{e}}_{n}\in E
span
E
iff, for all
{\vec{x}}\in E
,
\exists\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{n}\in\mathbb{R}

such that
{\vec{x}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\lambda_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
.

\bullet
A basis in
E
is a set
\{{\vec{e}}_{1},...,{\vec{e}}_{n}\}\subset E

made of
n
linearly independent vectors which span
E
, in which case the dimension of
E
is
n
.

A.1.3
Canonical basis

Consider the field
\mathbb{R}
of reals and the Cartesian product
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}=\mathbb{R}\times...\times\mathbb{R}
,
n
times.
The canonical basis is

{\vec{e}}_{1}=(1,0,...,0),\;...,\;{\vec{e}}_{n}=(0,...,0,1),

(A.1)

with
0=
the addition identity element used
n{-}1
times, and
1=
the multiplication identity element used once.

Remark A.2

The 3-D geometric space we live in has no canonical basis: What would the identity element
1
mean?
1 metre? 1 foot? And there is no “intrinsic” preferred direction to define
{\vec{e}}_{1}
.
So the Cartesian product
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}=\mathbb{R}\times...\times\mathbb{R}
and its canonical basis form an abstract mathematical model.

A.1.4
Cartesian basis

(René Descartes 1596-1650.)
Let
n=1,2,3
, let
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
be the usual affine space (space of points), and let
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}=({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}},+,.)
be the usual real vector space of bipoint vectors with its usual algebraic operations.

Let
p\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
, and let
({\vec{e}}_{i}(p))
be a basis at
p
.

A Cartesian basis in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
is a basis independent of
p
(the same at all
p
), and then
({\vec{e}}_{i}(p)){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}({\vec{e}}_{i})
.

Example of a non Cartesian basis:
The polar basis, see example
6.11
(polar coordinate system).

And a Euclidean basis is a particular Cartesian basis described in §
B.1
.

More generally, a Cartesian basis refers to
E^{n}=E\times...\times E
(
n
-times) where
E
is a dimension 1 vector space.

A.2
Representation of a vector relative to a basis

We give:

\bullet
the classical notation (non ambiguous), e.g. used by Arnold
[
5
]
and Germain
[
10
]
, and

\bullet
the duality notation (can be ambiguous because of misuses), e.g. used by Marsden and Hughes
[
14
]
.

Both classical and duality notation are equally good, but if you have any doubt, use the classical notations.

Definition A.3

Let
{\vec{x}}\in E
. Let
({\vec{e}}_{i})
be a basis in
E
.
The components of
{\vec{x}}
relative to the basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
are the
n
real numbers
x_{1},...,x_{n}
(classical notation) also named
x^{1},...,x^{n}
(duality notation) such that

{\vec{x}}=\underbrace{x_{1}{\vec{e}}_{1}+...+x_{n}{\vec{e}}_{n}}_{\hbox{\footnotesize clas{.}}}=\underbrace{x^{1}{\vec{e}}_{1}+...+x^{n}{\vec{e}}_{n}}_{\hbox{\footnotesize dual}},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\underbrace{\pmatrix{x_{1}\cr\vdots\cr x_{n}}}_{\hbox{\footnotesize clas{.}}}=\underbrace{\pmatrix{x^{1}\cr\vdots\cr x^{n}}}_{\hbox{\footnotesize dual}},

(A.2)

[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}
being the column matrix representing
{\vec{x}}
relative to the basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
.
(Of course
x_{i}=x^{i}
for all
i
.)
And the column matrix
[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}
is simply named
[{\vec{x}}]
if one chosen basis is imposed to all.
With the sum sign:

{\vec{x}}=\underbrace{{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}}_{\hbox{\footnotesize clas{.}}}=\underbrace{{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}}_{\hbox{\footnotesize dual}}\quad(={\sum_{J=1}^{n}}x_{J}{\vec{e}}_{J}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}x^{\alpha}{\vec{e}}_{\alpha}).

(A.3)

(The index in a summation is a dummy index, even if you do not write the sum sign
\sum
as can be done with Enstein’s convention:

{\vec{x}}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}x^{j}{\vec{e}}_{j}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}x^{j}{\vec{e}}_{j}=x^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}=x^{J}{\vec{e}}_{J}=x^{\alpha}{\vec{e}}_{\alpha}
.)

Example A.4

In
{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{2}}}
with
{\vec{x}}=3{\vec{e}}_{1}+4{\vec{e}}_{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{2}x_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{2}x^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
: We have
x_{1}{=}x^{1}{=}3
and
x_{2}{=}x^{2}{=}4
. And

[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=3[{\vec{e}}_{1}]_{|{\vec{e}}}+4[{\vec{e}}_{2}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\sum_{i=1}^{2}x_{i}[{\vec{e}}_{i}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\sum_{i=1}^{2}x^{i}[{\vec{e}}_{i}]_{|{\vec{e}}}
.
In particular, with
\delta^{i}_{j}=\delta_{ij}:=\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr=&1\hbox{ if }i{=}j\cr=&0\hbox{ if }i{\neq}j\crcr}}\,\right\}

the Kronecker symbols,

{\vec{e}}_{j}=\underbrace{{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\delta_{ij}{\vec{e}}_{i}}_{\hbox{\footnotesize clas{.}}}=\underbrace{{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\delta^{i}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}}_{\hbox{\footnotesize dual}},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad[{\vec{e}}_{1}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{1\cr 0\cr\vdots\cr 0},\;...,\;[{\vec{e}}_{n}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{0\cr\vdots\cr 0\cr 1},

(A.4)

that is, the components of
{\vec{e}}_{j}
are

\delta_{ij}
with classical notations, and

\delta^{i}_{j}
with duality notations. And the matrices
[{\vec{e}}_{j}]_{|{\vec{e}}}
mimic the use of theoretical Cartesian space
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}=\mathbb{R}\times...\times\mathbb{R}
and its canonical basis.

Remark A.5

The column matrix
[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}
is also called a “column vector”. NB: A “column vector” is not a vector, but just a matrix (a collection of real numbers). See the change of basis formula (
A.28
) where the same vector is represented by two “column vectors” (two column matrices).

A.3
Dual basis

Recall: Let
E
and
F
be vector spaces and
({\cal F}(E;F),+,.){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\cal F}(E;F)
be the usual real vector space of functions
with the internal addition
(f,g)\rightarrow f+g

defined by
(f+g)(x):=f(x)+g(x)

and the external multiplication
(\lambda,f)\rightarrow\lambda.f
defined by

(\lambda.f)(x):=\lambda(f(x))
, for all
f,g\in{\cal F}(E;F)
,
x\in E
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.
And
\lambda.f{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\lambda f
for all
f\in{\cal F}(E;F)
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.

A.3.1
Linear forms = “Covariant vectors”

Definition A.6

The set
E^{*}:={\cal L}(E;\mathbb{R})
of linear scalar valued functions is called the dual of
E
:

E^{*}:={\cal L}(E;\mathbb{R})=\hbox{the dual of $E$}.

(A.5)

And a linear scalar valued function
\ell\in E^{*}
is called a linear form.

More precisely,
E^{*}
as defined in (
A.5
) is the algebraic dual of
E
;
To define the topological dual usually needed with
L^{2}
functions in mechanics,
E
needs to be a Banach space
(a vector space equipped with a norm with which
E
is complete),
and
E^{*}
is then the set of continuous linear forms.
(If
E
is finite dimensional then
any linear form is continuous relative to any norm since all norms are equivalent in finite dimension.)

E^{*}
is a vector space: sub-space of
({\cal F}(R;\mathbb{R}),+,.)
(trivial).

Interpretation:
It answers the question: What does a function
E\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
do?
Answer: Like any function, it gives values to vectors:
\ell({\vec{u}})=
the value of
{\vec{u}}
through
\ell
.
That is, a
\ell\in E^{*}
is a measuring tool for vectors: If
{\vec{u}}\in E
then
\ell({\vec{u}})=
real value given by
\ell
.

Notation
: If
\ell\in E^{*}
then

\forall{\vec{u}}\in E,\quad\ell({\vec{u}}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\ell.{\vec{u}},

(A.6)

also written
\langle\ell,{\vec{u}}\rangle_{E^{*},E}
where
\langle.,.\rangle_{E^{*},E}
is the duality bracket: The dot in
\ell.{\vec{u}}
is “the distributivity dot” since linearity
\ell({\vec{u}}+\lambda{\vec{v}})=\ell({\vec{u}})+\lambda\ell({\vec{v}})
= distributivity

\ell.({\vec{u}}+\lambda{\vec{v}})=\ell.{\vec{u}}+\lambda\ell.{\vec{v}}
.

NB: The dot in
\ell.{\vec{u}}
is
not
an inner dot product (since
\ell\notin E
while
{\vec{u}}\in E
).

Definition A.7

A linear form
\ell
in
E^{*}
is also called a “covariant vector”;
Co-variant refers to:

1- The action of a function on a vector, cf. (
A.6
) (co-variant calculation), and

2- The change of coordinate formula
[\ell]_{{new}}=[\ell]_{|{old}}.P
, see (
A.28
) (covariant formula).

NB:

E^{*}
being a vector space, an element
\ell\in E^{*}
is indeed a vector.
But
E^{*}
has no existence if
E
has not been specified first since

E^{*}:={\cal L}(E;\mathbb{R})
. And
\ell\in E^{*}
can’t be confused with a vector
{\vec{u}}\in E
since there is no natural canonical isomorphism between
E
and
E^{*}
(no “intrinsic representation”), see §
T.2
.

Remark A.8

Misner–Thorne–Wheeler
[
16
]
, box 2.1, insist:
“Without it [the distinction between covariance and contravariance],
one cannot know whether a vector is meant or the very different object that is a linear form.”

A.3.2
Covariant dual basis (= the functions that give the components of a vector)

Notation:
If
{\vec{u}}_{1},...,{\vec{u}}_{k}
are vectors in
E
, then
{\rm Vect}\{{\vec{u}}_{1},...,{\vec{u}}_{k}\}:=
the vector space spanned by
{\vec{u}}_{1},...,{\vec{u}}_{k}
.

Let
E
be a finite dimensional vector space, and let
({\vec{e}}_{i})_{i=1,...,n}
be a basis in
E

Definition A.9

Let
i\in[1,n]_{\mathbb{N}}
.
The scalar projection on
{\rm Vect}\{{\vec{e}}_{i}\}
parallel to
{\rm Vect}\{{\vec{e}}_{1},...,{\vec{e}}_{i-1},{\vec{e}}_{i+1},...,{\vec{e}}_{n}\}
is the linear form named
\pi_{ei}\in E^{*}
with the classical notation, named
e^{i}\in E^{*}
with the duality notation, defined by, for all
i,j
,

\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil& $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&\hbox{clas. not.}:&\quad\pi_{ei}({\vec{e}}_{j})=\delta_{ij},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad\pi_{ei}.{\vec{e}}_{j}=\delta_{ij},\cr&\hbox{dual not.}:&\quad e^{i}({\vec{e}}_{j})=\delta^{i}_{j},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad e^{i}.{\vec{e}}_{j}=\delta^{i}_{j}.\cr}}\,\right.

(A.7)

Thus,
\pi_{ei}=e^{i}
being linear, if
{\vec{x}}\,\mathop{=}^{{\hbox{\footnotesize clas{.}}}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}\mathop{=}^{{\hbox{\footnotesize dual}}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
(classical or duality notations),
then (
A.7
) gives

\pi_{ei}.{\vec{x}}\,\mathop{=}^{{\hbox{\footnotesize clas{.}}}}x_{i}\quad=\quad x^{i}\mathop{=}^{{\hbox{\footnotesize dual}}}e^{i}.{\vec{x}},

(A.8)

i.e.
\pi_{ei}=e^{i}
gives
the
i
-th component of a vector
{\vec{x}}
relative to the basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
, see figure
A.1
.

Figure A.1:

Parallel projections:
\pi_{e1}({\vec{x}})=x_{1}
and
\pi_{e2}({\vec{x}})=x_{2}
(dual not.:
e^{1}({\vec{x}})=x^{1}
and
e^{2}({\vec{x}})=x^{2}
).

NB:
The dual basis
(\pi_{ei})
is intrinsic to
({\vec{e}}_{i})
; And
there can’t be any notion of orthogonality in
E
here since we can’t use a inner dot product:
The functions
\pi_{ei}=e^{i}
and vectors
{\vec{x}}
do not belong to a same vector space.

Proposition A.10

and definition of the dual basis.

(\pi_{ei})_{i=1,...,n}=(e^{i})_{i=1,...,n}
is a basis in
E^{*}
, called the (covariant) dual basis of the basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
.

Proof.
If
{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\lambda_{i}\pi_{ei}=0
, then
0=({\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\lambda_{i}\pi_{ei})({\vec{e}}_{j})={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\lambda_{i}\pi_{ei}({\vec{e}}_{j})={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\lambda_{i}\delta_{ij}=\lambda_{j}
for all
j
,
thus
(\pi_{ei})_{i=1,...,n}
is a family of
n
independent vectors in
E^{*}
.
Then let
\ell\in E^{*}
and
m=\sum_{i}(\ell.{\vec{e}}_{i})\pi_{ei}
. Thus
m\in E^{*}
(since
E^{*}
is a vector space),
and
m({\vec{e}}_{j})=\sum_{i}(\ell.{\vec{e}}_{i})(\pi_{ei}.{\vec{e}}_{j})=\sum_{i}(\ell.{\vec{e}}_{i})\delta_{ij}=(\ell.{\vec{e}}_{j})
,
thus
m=\ell
, thus
\ell=\sum_{i}(\ell.{\vec{e}}_{i})\pi_{ei}
, thus
{\rm Vect}\{(\pi_{ei})_{i=1,...,n}\}
span
E^{*}
;
Thus
(\pi_{ei})_{i=1,...,n}
is a basis in
E^{*}
; Thus
\dim E^{*}=n
. (Use duality notations if you prefer.)

Example A.11

Following example
1.1
.
The size of a child is represented on a wall by a bipoint vector
{\vec{u}}
.
And English observer chooses the foot as unit of length, represented by a vertical bipoint vector which he names
{\vec{e}}
.
And then defines the linear form
\pi_{e}:\vec{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by
\pi_{e}.{\vec{e}}=1
.
Thus
\pi_{e}
is a measuring instrument, which gives

s=\pi_{e}.{\vec{u}}=
the size of the child in foot, i.e.
{\vec{u}}=s{\vec{e}}
.

Exercice A.12

Let
({\vec{a}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
be bases and let
(\pi_{ai})
and
(\pi_{bi})
be the dual bases.
Let
\lambda\neq 0
. Prove:

\hbox{If }\;\forall i=1,...,n,\;{\vec{b}}_{i}=\lambda{\vec{a}}_{i},\quad\hbox{then}\quad\forall i=1,...,n,\;\pi_{bi}={1\over\lambda}\,\pi_{ai}.

(A.9)

(With duality notations,
b^{i}={1\over\lambda}\,a^{i}
.)

Answer
.
\pi_{bi}.{\vec{b}}_{j}=\delta_{ij}=\pi_{ai}.{\vec{a}}_{j}=\pi_{ai}.{{\vec{b}}_{j}\over\lambda}={1\over\lambda}\,\pi_{ai}.{\vec{b}}_{j}
for all
j
(since
\pi_{ai}
is linear),
thus
\pi_{bi}={1\over\lambda}\,\pi_{ai}
, true for all
i
.

A.3.3
Example: aeronautical units

Example A.13

International aeronautical units:
Horizontal length = nautical mile (NM),
altitude = English foot (ft).
Application: An air traffic controller chooses the point
O=
the position of its control tower,
and a plane
p
is located thanks to the bipoint vector
{\vec{x}}=\overrightarrow{{\cal O}p}
.
And the traffic controller chooses

{\vec{e}}_{1}=
the vector of length
1
NM oriented South (first runway),

{\vec{e}}_{2}=
the vector of length
1
NM oriented Southwest (second runway),

{\vec{e}}_{3}=
the vertical vector of length
1
ft.
Thus his referential is
{\cal R}=({\cal O},({\vec{e}}_{1},{\vec{e}}_{2},{\vec{e}}_{3}))
,
and his dual basis
(\pi_{e1},\pi_{e2},\pi_{e3})
is defined by
\pi_{ei}({\vec{e}}_{j})=\delta_{ij}
for all
i,j
, cf. (
A.7
). He writes
{\vec{x}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
, so that

x_{1}=\pi_{e1}({\vec{x}})=
the distance to the south in NM,

x_{2}=\pi_{e2}({\vec{x}})=
the distance to the southwest in NM,

x_{3}=\pi_{e3}({\vec{x}})=
the altitude in ft.

Here the basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is not a Euclidean basis.
This non Euclidean basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is however vital if you take a plane.
(A Euclidean basis is not essential to life…). See next remark
A.14
.

Remark A.14

The metre is the international unit for NASA that launched the Mars Climate Orbiter probe,
and the foot is the international vertical unit for aviation;
And for the Mars Climate Orbiter landing procedure,
NASA (uses the metre) asked Lockheed Martin (uses the foot) to do the computation.
Result? The Mars Climate Orbiter space probe burned in the Martian atmosphere
because of
\lambda\sim 3
times too high a speed during the landing procedure:
One metre is
\lambda\sim 3
times one foot, and someone forgot it…
Although NASA and Lockheed Martin used a Euclidean dot product… But not the same (one based on a metre, and one based on the foot). Objectivity and covariance can be useful…

A.3.4
Matrix representation of a linear form

Let
\ell\in E^{*}
, let
({\vec{e}}_{i})
be a basis: The components of
\ell
are the
n
reals

\ell_{i}:=\ell({\vec{e}}_{i})=\ell.{\vec{e}}_{i},\quad\hbox{and}\quad[\ell]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{\ell_{1}&...&\ell_{n}}

(A.10)

is the row matrix of
\ell
, called the matrix of
\ell
relative to
({\vec{e}}_{i})
. Thus, if
{\vec{x}}\in E

and
{\vec{x}}\,\mathop{=}^{{\hbox{\footnotesize clas{.}}}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}\mathop{=}^{{\hbox{\footnotesize dual}}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
, then

\ell.{\vec{x}}\,\mathop{=}^{{\hbox{\footnotesize clas{.}}}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\ell_{i}x_{i}\mathop{=}^{{\hbox{\footnotesize dual}}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\ell_{i}x^{i}=[\ell]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}

(A.11)

with usual matrix computation rules (a
1*n
matrix times a
n*1
matrix).

In particular for the dual basis
(\pi_{ei})=(e^{i})
(classical and duality notations),

[\pi_{ej}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[e^{j}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=(0\;\;...\;\;0\;\;\underbrace{1}_{\makebox[28.45274pt]{\footnotesize\rm$j$th position}}\;\;0\;\;...\;\;0)\quad(\hbox{= row matrix }=[{\vec{e}}_{j}]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}).

(A.12)

Thus we have, with classical and duality notations,

\ell\,\mathop{=}^{{\hbox{\footnotesize clas{.}}}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\ell_{i}\,\pi_{ei}\mathop{=}^{{\hbox{\footnotesize dual}}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\ell_{i}\,e^{i}.

(A.13)

Remark A.15

Relative to a basis,
a vector is represented by a column matrix, cf. (
A.2
),
and a linear form by a row matrix, cf. (
A.10
). This enables:

\bullet
The use of matrix calculation to compute

\ell.{\vec{x}}=[\ell]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}
, cf. (
A.11
), not to be confused with an inner dot product calculation

{\vec{x}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{\vec{y}}
relative to an inner dot product in
E
for
{\vec{x}},{\vec{y}}\in E
.

\bullet
Not to confuse the “nature of objects”: Relative to a basis,
a (contravariant) vector is a mathematical object represented by a column matrix,
while a linear form (covariant vector) is a mathematical object represented by a row matrix.
Cf. remark
A.8
.

A.3.5
Example: Thermodynamic

Consider the Cartesian space
{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{2}}}=\{(T,P)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\}=\{
(temperature,pressure)
\}
.
There is no meaningful inner dot product in this
{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{2}}}
: What would
\sqrt{T^{2}{+}P^{2}}
mean (Pythagoras: Can you add Kelvin degrees and kg/(m·
s^{2}
)? Thus, in thermodynamics, the (covariant) dual bases are the main ingredient for calculations.

E.g., in the Cartesian product
{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{2}}}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
consider the basis
({\vec{E}}_{1}{=}(1,0),{\vec{E}}_{2}{=}(0,1))
(after a choice of temperature and pressure units); Let
{\vec{X}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{2}}}
,
{\vec{X}}=T{\vec{E}}_{1}+P{\vec{E}}_{2}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}(T,P)
, and let
(\pi_{E1},\pi_{E2})=(E^{1},E^{2}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}(dT,dP)
be the (covariant) dual basis.
The first principle of thermodynamics tells that the density
\alpha
of internal energy is an exact differential form:
\exists U\in C^{1}({\vec{\mathbb{R}^{2}}};\mathbb{R})
s.t.
\alpha=dU
. So, at any
{\vec{X}}_{0}=(T_{0},P_{0})
,

\alpha({\vec{X}}_{0})=dU({\vec{X}}_{0})={\partial U\over\partial T}({\vec{X}}_{0})\,dT+{\partial U\over\partial P}({\vec{X}}_{0})\,dP\quad\hbox{and}\quad[dU({\vec{X}}_{0})]_{|{\vec{E}}}=\pmatrix{{\partial U\over\partial T}({\vec{X}}_{0})&{\partial U\over\partial P}({\vec{X}}_{0})}

(A.14)

(row matrix).
And we have the first order Taylor expansion in the vicinity of
{\vec{X}}_{0}
,

\displaystyle{U({\vec{X}}_{0}+\delta{\vec{X}})=}

\displaystyle{{}U({\vec{X}}_{0})+dU({\vec{X}}_{0}).\delta{\vec{X}}+o(\delta{\vec{X}})}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}U(T_{0},P_{0})+\delta T{\partial U\over\partial T}(T_{0},P_{0})+\delta P{\partial U\over\partial T}(T_{0},P_{0})+o((\delta T,\delta P)).}

(A.15)

Matrix computation:
Column matrices for vectors, row matrices for linear forms:

[{\vec{E}}_{1}]_{|{\vec{E}}}=\pmatrix{1\cr 0},\quad[{\vec{E}}_{2}]_{|{\vec{E}}}=\pmatrix{0\cr 1},\quad[{\vec{X}}_{0}]_{|{\vec{E}}}=\pmatrix{T_{0}\cr P_{0}},\quad[\delta{\vec{X}}]_{|{\vec{E}}}=\pmatrix{\delta T\cr\delta P},

(A.16)

[E^{1}]_{|{\vec{E}}}=[dT]_{|{\vec{E}}}=\pmatrix{1&0},\quad[E^{2}]_{|{\vec{E}}}=[dP]_{|{\vec{E}}}=\pmatrix{0&1},\quad[dU]_{|{\vec{E}}}=\pmatrix{{\partial U\over\partial T}&{\partial U\over\partial P}}

(A.17)

give

\displaystyle{dU({\vec{X}}_{0}).\delta{\vec{X}}=}

\displaystyle{{}\pmatrix{{\partial U\over\partial T}({\vec{X}}_{0})&{\partial U\over\partial P}({\vec{X}}_{0})}.\pmatrix{\delta T\cr\delta P}={\partial U\over\partial T}({\vec{X}}_{0})\delta T+{\partial U\over\partial P}({\vec{X}}_{0})\delta P.}

(A.18)

This is a “covariant calculation” (in particular no inner dot product has been used).

A.4
Einstein convention

A.4.1
Definition

When you work with components (after a choice of a basis), the goal is to visually differentiate a linear form from a vector (to visually differentiate covariance from contravariance).
Framework: a finite dimension vector space
E
,
\dim E=n
, and duality notations.

Einstein Convention:

1.

A basis in
E
(contravariant) is written with bottom indices: E.g.,
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is a basis in
E
.

2.

A vector
{\vec{x}}\in E
(contravariant) has its components relative to
({\vec{e}}_{i})
(quantification) written with top indices:
{\vec{x}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,
and is represented by the column matrix
[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{x^{1}\cr\vdots\cr x^{n}}
.
(Classical notations:
{\vec{x}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
, and column matrix of
x_{i}
.)

3.

A basis in
E^{*}={\cal L}(E;\mathbb{R})
(covariant) is written with top indices: E.g.,
(e^{i})\in{E^{*}}^{n}
is the dual basis of the basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
. (Classical notations:
(\pi_{ei})
.)

4.

A linear form
\ell\in E^{*}
(covariant) has its components relative to
(e^{i})
(quantification) written with bottom indices:
\ell={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\ell_{i}e^{i}
,
and its matrix representation is the row matrix
[\ell]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{\ell_{1}&...&\ell_{n}}
.

5.

You can also omit the sum sign
\sum
when there are repeated indices at a different position; E.g.
{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}x^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
, and

{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{L^{i}{}_{j}}{\vec{e}}_{i}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}L^{i}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}
. In fact, before computers and word processors, to print
{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}
was not easy. But with
L
a
T
e
X
this is no more a problem, so in this manuscript the sum sign
\sum
is not omitted (and some confusions are avoided).

Remark A.16

Einstein’s convention is not mandatory. E.g. Arnold doesn’t use it when he doesn’t need it, or when it makes reading difficult, or when it induces misunderstandings. In classical mechanics, Einstein’s convention may induce more confusion than understandings, and may be misused… so it is better
not
to use it:
Golden rule: Use classical notations when in doubt.

A.4.2
Do not mistake yourself

1.

Einstein’s convention is just meant not to confuse a linear function with a vector.

2.

It only deals with quantification relative to a basis.

3.

Classical notations are as good as duality notations, even you are told that classical notations cannot detect obvious errors in component manipulations… But duality notations can be misused in classical mechanics (cf. the paradigmatic example of the vectorial dual basis, correctly treated at §
F.7
); And thus add confusion to the confusion.

4.

The convention does not admit shortcuts; E.g. with a metric:
g({\vec{u}},{\vec{v}})={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}g_{ij}u^{i}v^{j}
shows the observer dependence on a choice of a basis thanks to the
g_{ij}
; And even if
g_{ij}=\delta_{ij}
you can
not
write
g({\vec{u}},{\vec{v}})={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}u^{i}v^{j}
: You have to write
g({\vec{u}},{\vec{v}})={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}g_{ij}u^{i}v^{j}
: Unmissable in physics because you need to see the metric and bases in use.

5.

Golden rule: Return to classical notations if in doubt.
(Einstein’s convention can add confusions, untruths, misinterpretations, absurdities, misuses…)

A.5
Change of basis formulas

E
being a finite dimension vector space,
\dim E=n
, let
({{\vec{e}}_{old,i}})
and
({{\vec{e}}_{new,i}})
be two bases in
E
,
and let
(\pi_{{old},i})
and
(\pi_{{new},i})
be the dual bases in
E^{*}
, written
({e^{i}_{old}})
and
({e^{i}_{new}})
with duality notations.

A.5.1
Change of basis endomorphism and transition matrix

Definition A.17

The change of basis endomorphism
{\cal P}\in{\cal L}(E;E)
from
({{\vec{e}}_{old,i}})
to
({{\vec{e}}_{new,i}})
is
the endomorphism (= the linear map
E\rightarrow E
) defined by, for all
j\in[1,n]_{\mathbb{N}}
,

\framebox{$\displaystyle{\cal P}.{{\vec{e}}_{old,j}}={{\vec{e}}_{new,j}}$}.

(A.19)

Definition A.18

The transition matrix from
({{\vec{e}}_{old,i}})
to
({{\vec{e}}_{new,i}})
is the matrix

P:=[{\cal P}]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{old}}}=[P_{ij}]
of the endomorphism
{\cal P}
relative to the basis
({{\vec{e}}_{old,i}})
,
i.e. defined by, for all
j
,

{{\vec{e}}_{new,j}}={\cal P}.{{\vec{e}}_{old,j}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}P_{ij}\,{{\vec{e}}_{old,i}},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad[{{\vec{e}}_{new,j}}]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{old}}}=P.[{{\vec{e}}_{old,j}}]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{old}}}=\pmatrix{\displaystyle P_{1j}\cr\vdots\cr\displaystyle P_{nj}},

(A.20)

i.e.,
[{{\vec{e}}_{new,j}}]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{old}}}
is the
j
-th column of
P=[{\cal P}]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{old}}}
.
Duality notations:
{{\vec{e}}_{new,j}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{P^{i}{}_{j}}\,{{\vec{e}}_{old,i}}
and
P:=[{\cal P}]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{old}}}=[{P^{i}{}_{j}}]
.

Apart from the classical and notations, you may find other “component type” notations:

{{\vec{e}}_{new,j}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}P_{ij}\,{{\vec{e}}_{old,i}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}(P_{j})_{i}\,{{\vec{e}}_{old,i}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{P^{i}{}_{j}}\,{{\vec{e}}_{old,i}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}(P_{j})^{i}\,{{\vec{e}}_{old,i}},

(A.21)

i.e.
P_{ij}=(P_{j})_{i}={P^{i}{}_{j}}=(P_{j})^{i}
are four notations for the
i
-th component of
{\vec{e}}_{j}
,
i.e.

[{{\vec{e}}_{new,j}}]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{old}}}=\pmatrix{\displaystyle P_{1j}\cr\vdots\cr\displaystyle P_{nj}}=\pmatrix{\displaystyle(P_{j})_{1}\cr\vdots\cr\displaystyle(P_{j})_{n}}=\pmatrix{\displaystyle P^{1}{}_{j}\cr\vdots\cr\displaystyle P^{n}{}_{j}}=\pmatrix{\displaystyle(P_{j})^{1}\cr\vdots\cr\displaystyle(P_{j})^{n}}\quad\hbox{(= the $j$-th column of $P$)}.

(A.22)

A.5.2
Inverse of the transition matrix

The inverse endomorphism
{\cal Q}:={\cal P}^{-1}\in{\cal L}(E;E)
, cf. (
A.19
), is given by, for all
j\in[1,n]_{\mathbb{N}}
,

{{\vec{e}}_{old,j}}={\cal Q}.{{\vec{e}}_{new,j}}\quad(={\cal P}^{-1}.{{\vec{e}}_{new,j}}),

(A.23)

i.e.
{\cal Q}
is change of basis endomorphism from
({{\vec{e}}_{new,i}})
to
({{\vec{e}}_{old,i}})
.
And
\displaystyle Q:=[{\cal Q}]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{new}}}=[Q_{ij}]
is the transition matrix from
({{\vec{e}}_{new,i}})
to
({{\vec{e}}_{old,i}})
:

{{\vec{e}}_{old,j}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}Q_{ij}{{\vec{e}}_{new,i}},\quad[{{\vec{e}}_{old,j}}]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{new}}}=\pmatrix{Q_{1j}\cr\vdots\cr Q_{nj}}.

(A.24)

i.e.
{\cal Q}
is change of basis endomorphism from
({{\vec{e}}_{new,i}})
to
({{\vec{e}}_{old,i}})
.

Use other notation if you prefer:
Q_{ij}=(Q_{j})_{i}={Q^{i}{}_{j}}=(Q_{j})^{i}

Proposition A.19

Q=P^{-1}.

(A.25)

Proof.
{{\vec{e}}_{new,j}}={\cal P}.{{\vec{e}}_{old,j}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}P_{ij}{{\vec{e}}_{old,i}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}P_{ij}({\sum_{k=1}^{n}}Q_{ki}{{\vec{e}}_{new,k}})={\sum_{k=1}^{n}}({\sum_{i=1}^{n}}Q_{ki}P_{ij}){{\vec{e}}_{new,k}}={\sum_{k=1}^{n}}(Q.P)_{kj}{{\vec{e}}_{new,k}}
for all
j
,
thus
(Q.P)_{kj}=\delta_{kj}
for all
j,k
. Hence
Q.P=I
, i.e. (
A.25
).

Exercice A.20

Prove
\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&[{\cal P}]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{old}}}=[{\cal P}]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{new}}}=P,\cr&[{\cal Q}]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{new}}}=[{\cal Q}]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{old}}}=Q,\cr}}\,\right\}
,
i.e.

\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&\textstyle{\cal P}.{{\vec{e}}_{new,j}}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}P_{ij}{{\vec{e}}_{new,i}}\quad(={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}{P^{i}{}_{j}}{{\vec{e}}_{new,i}}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}(P_{j})^{i}{{\vec{e}}_{new,i}}),\cr&\textstyle{\cal Q}.{{\vec{e}}_{old,j}}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}Q_{ij}{{\vec{e}}_{old,i}}\quad(={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}{Q^{i}{}_{j}}{{\vec{e}}_{old,i}}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}(Q_{j})^{i}{{\vec{e}}_{old,i}}).\cr}}\,\right.

(A.26)

Answer
.
Z=[Z_{ij}]=[{\cal P}]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{new}}}
means
{\cal P}.{{\vec{e}}_{new,j}}=\sum_{i}Z_{ij}{{\vec{e}}_{new,i}}
, i.e.
{{\vec{e}}_{new,j}}={\cal Q}.({\sum_{i=1}^{n}}Z_{ij}{{\vec{e}}_{new,i}})={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}Z_{ij}{\cal Q}.{{\vec{e}}_{new,i}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}Z_{ij}({\sum_{k=1}^{n}}Q_{ki}{{\vec{e}}_{new,k}})={\sum_{k=1}^{n}}({\sum_{i=1}^{n}}Q_{ki}Z_{ij}){{\vec{e}}_{new,k}}={\sum_{k=1}^{n}}(Q.Z)_{kj}{{\vec{e}}_{new,k}}
for all
j
,
thus
(Q.Z)_{kj}=\delta_{kj}
for all
j,k
, thus
Q.Z=I
, thus
Z=P
.
Idem for
Q
, thus (
A.26
).

Remark A.21

P^{T}\neq P^{-1}
in general.
E.g.,
({{\vec{e}}_{old,i}})=({\vec{a}}_{i})
is a foot-built Euclidean basis,
and
({{\vec{e}}_{new,i}})=({\vec{b}}_{i})
is a metre-built Euclidean basis,
and
{\vec{b}}_{i}=\lambda{\vec{a}}_{i}
for all
i
(the basis are “aligned”), so
P=\lambda I
; Thus
P^{T}=\lambda I
and
P^{-1}={1\over\lambda}I\neq P^{T}
,
since
\lambda={1\over 0.3048}\neq 1
.
Thus it is essential not to confuse
P^{T}
and
P^{-1}
(not to confuse covariance with contravariance), cf. e.g. the Mars Climate Orbiter crash (remark
A.14
).

A.5.3
Change of dual basis

Proposition A.22

(\pi_{{new},i})
and
(\pi_{{old},i})
being the dual bases of
({{\vec{e}}_{new,i}})
and
({{\vec{e}}_{old,i}})
, for all
i\in[1,n]_{\mathbb{N}}
,

\pi_{{new},i}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}Q_{ij}\pi_{{old},i},\quad\hbox{and}\quad[\pi_{{new},i}]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{old}}}=\pmatrix{Q_{i1}&...&Q_{in}}\quad\hbox{($i$-th row of $Q$)},

(A.27)

to compare with (
A.20
) (matrices of linear forms are row matrices).

Duality notations:
{e^{i}_{new}}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}{Q^{i}{}_{j}}{e^{j}_{old}}
and
[{e^{i}_{new}}]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{old}}}=\pmatrix{Q^{i}{}_{1}&...&Q^{i}{}_{n}}
.

Proof.
\pi_{{new},i}({{\vec{e}}_{old,k}})\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdefQ0})}\pi_{{new},i}(\sum_{j}Q_{jk}{{\vec{e}}_{new,j}})=\sum_{j}Q_{jk}\,\pi_{{new},i}({{\vec{e}}_{new,j}})=\sum_{j}Q_{jk}\,\delta_{ij}=Q_{ik}
,
and

\sum_{j}Q_{ij}\pi_{{old},j}({{\vec{e}}_{old,k}})=\sum_{j}Q_{ij}\delta_{jk}=Q_{ik}
,
true for all
i,k
,
thus
\pi_{{new},i}=\sum_{j}Q_{ij}
,
i.e. (
A.27
)

A.5.4
Change of coordinate system for vectors and linear forms

Proposition A.23

Let
{\vec{x}}\in E
and
\ell\in{E^{*}}
. Then

\displaystyle{{}\bullet\;[{\vec{x}}]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{new}}}=P^{-1}.[{\vec{x}}]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{old}}}\quad\hbox{(contravariance formula for vectors: between column matrices)},}

\displaystyle{{}\bullet\;[\ell]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{new}}}=[\ell]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{old}}}.P\qquad\hbox{(covariance formula for linear forms: between row matrices)}.}

(A.28)

And the scalar value
\ell.{\vec{x}}
is computed indifferently with one or the other basis (objective result):

\ell.{\vec{x}}=[\ell]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{old}}}.[{\vec{x}}]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{old}}}=[\ell]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{new}}}.[{\vec{x}}]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{new}}}.

(A.29)

Proof.
Let
{\vec{x}}=\sum_{j}x_{j}{{\vec{e}}_{old,j}}=\sum_{i}y_{i}{{\vec{e}}_{new,i}}
.
We have
{\vec{x}}=\sum_{j}x_{j}{{\vec{e}}_{old,j}}=\sum_{j}x_{j}({\sum_{i=1}^{n}}Q_{ij}{{\vec{e}}_{new,i}})=\sum_{ij}Q_{ij}x_{j}{{\vec{e}}_{new,i}}
,
thus
y_{i}=\sum_{j}Q_{ij}x_{j}
for all
i
, thus (
A.28
)
1
.

And

\ell=\sum_{j}m_{j}\pi_{{new},j}=\sum_{i}\ell_{i}\pi_{{old},i}\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdefP2})}\sum_{ij}\ell_{i}P_{ij}\pi_{{new},j}

gives
m_{j}=\sum_{i}\ell_{i}P_{ij}
for all
j
, thus (
A.28
)
2
.
(Use duality notations if you prefer.)

Thus

[\ell]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{new}}}.[{\vec{x}}]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{new}}}=([\ell]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{old}}}.P).(P^{-1}.[{\vec{x}}]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{old}}})=[\ell]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{old}}}.[{\vec{x}}]_{|{{\vec{e}}_{old}}}
,
hence (
A.29
).

Notation:

(
A.28
) and
{\vec{x}}=\sum_{j}x_{j}{{\vec{e}}_{old,j}}=\sum_{i}y_{i}{{\vec{e}}_{new,i}}
give
y_{i}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}Q_{ij}x_{j}
,
which means:
y_{i}
is the function defined by
y_{i}(x_{1},...,x_{n})={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}Q_{ij}x_{j}
,
thus
Q_{ij}={\partial y_{i}\over\partial x_{j}}(x_{1},...,x_{n})
; Similarly with
P_{ij}
; Which is written

Q_{ij}={\partial y_{i}\over\partial x_{j}},\quad\hbox{and}\quad P_{ij}={\partial x_{i}\over\partial y_{j}}.

(A.30)

(Use duality notations if you prefer, e.g.
{Q^{i}{}_{j}}={\partial y^{i}\over\partial x^{j}}
.)

A.6
Bidual basis (and contravariance)

Definition A.24

The dual of
E^{*}
is
{E^{**}}:=(E^{*})^{*}={\cal L}(E^{*};\mathbb{R})
and is named the bidual of
E
.

{E^{**}}
is also called the space of contravariant vectors = the space of directional derivatives.

Definition A.25

Let
({\vec{e}}_{i})
be a basis in
E
,
let
(\pi_{ei})
be its dual basis (basis in
{E^{*}}
).
The dual basis
(\partial_{i})
of
(\pi_{ei})
is called the bidual basis of
({\vec{e}}_{i})
.
(Duality notations:
(\pi_{ei})=(e^{i})
.)

(The notation
\partial_{i}
refers to the derivation in the direction
{\vec{e}}_{i}
:

\partial_{i}(df({\vec{x}}))=df({\vec{x}}).{\vec{e}}_{i}={\partial f\over\partial x_{i}}({\vec{x}})
, see §
S.1
.)

Thus, the linear form
\partial_{i}\in{E^{**}}={\cal L}(E^{*};\mathbb{R})
are characterized by, for all
j
,

\partial_{i}.\pi_{ej}=\delta_{ij}=\pi_{ej}.{\vec{e}}_{i},\quad\hbox{so}\quad\ell={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\ell_{i}\pi_{ei}\quad\hbox{iff}\quad\ell_{i}=\partial_{i}.\ell\;\;(=\ell.{\vec{e}}_{i}).

(A.31)

Indeed,
\partial_{i}(\ell)=\partial_{i}({\sum_{j=1}^{n}}\ell_{j}\pi_{ej})={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}\ell_{j}\partial_{i}(\pi_{ej})={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}\ell_{j}\delta_{ij}=\ell_{i}
. (Duality notation:
\partial_{i}.e^{j}=\delta^{j}_{i}=e^{j}.{\vec{e}}_{i}
and
\ell={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\ell_{i}e^{i}
.)

Remark:

With the natural canonical isomorphism

{\cal J}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr E&\rightarrow E^{**}={\cal L}({E^{*}};\mathbb{R})\cr{\vec{u}}&\rightarrow{\cal J}({\vec{u}}),\;\;\hbox{where}\;\;{\cal J}({\vec{u}}).\ell:=\ell.{\vec{u}},\;\;\forall\ell\in{E^{*}}\crcr}}\,\right\}

see (
T.9
),
we can identify
{\vec{u}}
and
{\cal J}({\vec{u}})
(observer independent identification), thus
\partial_{i}={\cal J}({\vec{e}}_{i}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{e}}_{i}
, and (
A.31
) reads (usual notation in differential geometry)

{\vec{e}}_{i}.\pi_{ej}=\delta_{ij}
and
\ell_{i}={\vec{e}}_{i}.\ell
.

A.7
Bilinear forms

A.7.1
Definition

Let
E
and
F
be vector spaces.

Definition A.26

\bullet
A bilinear form is a
2
-multilinear form
\beta(\cdot,\cdot):\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr E\times F&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})&\rightarrow\beta({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})\crcr}}\,\right\}
.

So,
\beta({\vec{u}}_{1}+\lambda{\vec{u}}_{2},{\vec{w}})=\beta({\vec{u}}_{1},{\vec{w}})+\lambda\beta({\vec{u}}_{2},{\vec{w}})
and
\beta({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}}_{1}+\lambda{\vec{w}}_{2})=\beta({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}}_{1})+\lambda\beta({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}}_{2})
for all
{\vec{u}},{\vec{u}}_{1},{\vec{u}}_{2}\in E
,
{\vec{w}},{\vec{w}}_{1},{\vec{w}}_{2}\in F
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.

\bullet

{\cal L}(E,F;\mathbb{R})
is the set of bilinear forms
E\times F\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.

\bullet
If
(\ell,m)\in E^{*}\times F^{*}
, then the bilinear form
\ell\otimes m\in{\cal L}(E,F;\mathbb{R})
is defined by

(\ell\otimes m)({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})=\ell({\vec{u}})m({\vec{w}})\quad(=(\ell.{\vec{u}})(m.{\vec{w}}))

(A.32)

for all
({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})\in E\times F
, and is called an elementary bilinear form.

A.7.2
The transposed of a bilinear form

(Warning: Not to be confused with the subjective definition of a transposed of a linear map which requires inner dot products to be defined, see e.g. (
A.54
).)

Definition A.27

If
\beta\in{\cal L}(E,F;\mathbb{R})
then its transposed is the bilinear form
\beta^{T}\in{\cal L}(F,E;\mathbb{R})
defined by,
for all
({\vec{w}},{\vec{u}})\in F\times E
,

\beta^{T}({\vec{w}},{\vec{u}})=\beta({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}}).

(A.33)

(This definition is observer independent: no basis or inner dot product is required in this definition.)

A.7.3
Symmetric and definite positive bilinear forms

Definition A.28

Here
F=E
(no choice), and
\beta\in{\cal L}(E,E;\mathbb{R})
.

\bullet

\beta
is semi-positive, iff for all
{\vec{u}}\in E
,

\beta({\vec{u}},{\vec{u}})\geq 0.

(A.34)

\bullet

\beta
is definite positive, iff for all
{\vec{u}}\neq\vec{0}
,

\beta({\vec{u}},{\vec{u}})>0.

(A.35)

\bullet

\beta
is symmetric iff
\beta^{T}=\beta
, i.e., for all
{\vec{u}},{\vec{v}}\in E
,

\beta({\vec{u}},{\vec{v}})=\beta({\vec{v}},{\vec{u}}).

(A.36)

A.7.4
Inner dot product, and metric

Definition A.29

\bullet
An “inner dot product” (or “scalar inner dot product”,
or “inner scalar product”, or “inner product”)
in a vector space
E
is a bilinear form
\beta{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}g{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}g(\cdot,\cdot)\in{\cal L}(E,E;\mathbb{R})
which is symmetric and definite positive.
And then (for inner dot products)

g(\cdot,\cdot){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\cdot\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,\cdot,\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad g({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})=({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})_{g}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{u}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,{\vec{w}},\;\;\forall{\vec{u}},{\vec{w}}\in E.

(A.37)

\bullet
Then two vectors
{\vec{u}},{\vec{w}}\in E
are
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-orthogonal iff
({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})_{g}=0
.

\bullet
And the associated norm with
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
is the function
||.||_{g}:E\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
given by, for all
{\vec{u}}\in E
,

||{\vec{u}}||_{g}=\sqrt{({\vec{u}},{\vec{u}})_{g}}.

(A.38)

(To prove that it is a norm, use the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality (
A.39
).)

\bullet
An “semi-inner dot product”
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
(or “semi-scalar inner dot product”)
in a vector space
E
is a bilinear form
\beta{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}g(\cdot,\cdot)\in{\cal L}(E,E;\mathbb{R})
which is symmetric and semi-positive.
And the associated semi-norm is given by (
A.38
).

Proposition A.30

(Cauchy–Schwarz inequality.)
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
being an inner dot product in
E
,

\forall{\vec{u}},{\vec{w}}\in E,\quad|({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})_{g}|\leq||{\vec{u}}||_{g}||{\vec{w}}||_{g}.

(A.39)

And
|({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})_{g}|=||{\vec{u}}||_{g}||{\vec{w}}||_{g}
iff
{\vec{u}}
and
{\vec{w}}
are parallel.

Proof.
Let
p(\lambda)=||{\vec{u}}{+}\lambda{\vec{w}}||_{g}^{2}=({\vec{u}}{+}\lambda{\vec{w}},{\vec{u}}{+}\lambda{\vec{w}})_{g}
,
so
p(\lambda)=a\lambda^{2}+b\lambda+c
where
a=||{\vec{w}}||_{g}^{2}
,
b=2({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})_{g}
and
c=||{\vec{u}}||^{2}_{g}
.
With
p(\lambda)\geq 0
(since
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
is positive), we get
b^{2}-4ac\geq 0
, thus (
A.39
),
and
p(\lambda)=0
iff
{\vec{u}}{+}\lambda{\vec{w}}=0
.

Definition A.31

(Metric.)
With
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
our usual affine geometric space,
n=1
,
2
or
3
, and
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}=
the usual associated vector space made of bipoint vectors.
Let
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
be open in
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
.
A metric in
\Omega
is a
C^{\infty}
function

g:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\Omega&\rightarrow{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}},{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}};\mathbb{R})\cr p&\rightarrow g(p){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}g_{p}\crcr}}\,\right\}

such that
g_{p}
is an inner dot product in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
at each
p\in\Omega
.

Particular Case: When the
g_{p}
is independent of
p
(general case in continuum mechanics),
a metric is simply called a inner dot product (e.g. a Euclidean metric is called a Euclidean dot product).

(In a differentiable manifold
\Omega
, a metric is a
C^{\infty}

{0\choose 2}
tensor
g
s.t.
g(p)
is an inner dot product at each
p\in\Omega
.
A Riemannian metric is a metric s.t.
g(p)
is a Euclidean dot product at each
p\in\Omega
.)

A.7.5
Quantification: Matrice
[\beta_{ij}]
and tensorial representation

\dim E=n
,
\dim F=m
,
\beta\in{\cal L}(E,F;\mathbb{R})
,

({\vec{a}}_{i})
is a basis in
E
which dual basis is
({\pi_{ai}})
,
({\vec{b}}_{i})
is a basis in
F
which dual basis is
({\pi_{bi}})
.
(With duality notations,
({\pi_{ai}})=(a^{i})
and
({\pi_{bi}})=(b^{i})
.)

Definition A.32

The components of
\beta\in{\cal L}(E,F;\mathbb{R})
relative to the bases
({\vec{a}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
are the
nm
reals

\beta_{ij}:=\beta({\vec{a}}_{i},{\vec{b}}_{j}),\quad\hbox{and}\quad[\beta]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}=[\beta_{ij}]_{i=1,...,n\atop j=1,...,m}

(A.40)

is the matrix of
\beta
relative to the bases
({\vec{a}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
, simply written
[\beta_{ij}]
if the bases are implicit.

And if
F=E
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})=({\vec{a}}_{i})
then
[\beta]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}[\beta]_{|{\vec{a}}}
.

Proposition A.33

A bilinear form
\beta\in{\cal L}(E,F;\mathbb{R})
is known as soon as the
nm
scalars
\beta_{ij}=\beta({\vec{a}}_{i},{\vec{b}}_{j})
are known, and, for all
({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})\in E\times F
,

\beta({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})=[{\vec{u}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}{}^{T}.[\beta]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}.[{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{b}}},\quad\hbox{written}\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle\beta({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})=[{\vec{u}}]^{T}.[\beta].[{\vec{w}}]$},

(A.41)

so

\beta={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\sum_{j=1}^{m}}\beta_{ij}\pi_{ai}\otimes\pi_{bj},

(A.42)

and a basis in
{\cal L}(E,F;\mathbb{R})
is made of the
nm
functions
\pi_{ai}\otimes\pi_{bj}
, and
\dim{\cal L}(E,F;\mathbb{R})=nm
.
(Duality notations:
\beta={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\sum_{j=1}^{m}}\beta_{ij}a^{i}\otimes b^{j}
.)

Proof.
\beta
being bilinear,
{\vec{u}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}u_{i}{\vec{a}}_{i}
and
{\vec{w}}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}w_{j}{\vec{b}}_{j}
give

\beta({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}u_{i}w_{j}\beta({\vec{a}}_{i},{\vec{b}}_{j})={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}u_{i}\beta_{ij}w_{j}=([{\vec{u}}]_{|{\vec{a}}})^{T}.[\beta]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}.[{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}
,
thus (
A.41
). In particular, if the
\beta_{ij}
are known,
then
b
is known.
And
({\pi_{ai}}\otimes{\pi_{bj}})({\vec{a}}_{k},{\vec{b}}_{\ell})\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdefelemb})}}({\pi_{ai}}.{\vec{a}}_{k})({\pi_{bj}}.{\vec{b}}_{\ell})=\delta_{ik}\delta_{j\ell}

(all the elements of the matrix
[{\pi_{ai}}\otimes{\pi_{bj}}]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}
are zero except the element at the intersection of row
i
and column
j
which is equal to
1
).
And
({\pi_{ai}}\otimes{\pi_{bj}})({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdefelemb})}}({\pi_{ai}}.{\vec{u}})({\pi_{bj}}.{\vec{w}})=u_{i}w_{j}
, thus

\beta({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}\beta_{ij}u_{i}w_{j}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}\beta_{ij}({\pi_{ai}}\otimes{\pi_{bj}})({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})
, thus

\beta:={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}\beta_{ij}({\pi_{ai}}\otimes{\pi_{bj}})
, thus the
{\pi_{ai}}\otimes{\pi_{bj}}
span
{\cal L}(E,F;\mathbb{R})
.
And
\sum_{ij}\lambda_{ij}({\pi_{ai}}\otimes{\pi_{bj}})=0
implies

0=(\sum_{ij}\lambda_{ij}({\pi_{ai}}\otimes{\pi_{bj}}))({\vec{a}}_{k},{\vec{b}}_{\ell})=\sum_{ij}\lambda_{ij}({\pi_{ai}}\otimes{\pi_{bj}})({\vec{a}}_{k},{\vec{b}}_{\ell})=\lambda_{k\ell}=0
for all
k,\ell
;
Thus the
{\pi_{ai}}\otimes{\pi_{bj}}
are independent.
Thus
({\pi_{ai}}\otimes{\pi_{bj}})
is a basis in
{\cal L}(E,F;\mathbb{R})
and
\dim({\cal L}(E,F;\mathbb{R}))=nm
.
(Duality notations:
\beta({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}\beta_{ij}u^{i}w^{j}
and
\beta:={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}\beta_{ij}\,a^{i}\otimes b^{j}
.)

Example A.34

\dim E=\dim F=2
.

[\beta]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}=\pmatrix{1&2\cr 0&3}

means
\beta({\vec{a}}_{1},{\vec{b}}_{1})=\beta_{11}=1
,
\beta({\vec{a}}_{1},{\vec{b}}_{2})=\beta_{12}=2
,
\beta({\vec{a}}_{2},{\vec{b}}_{1})=\beta_{21}=0
,
\beta({\vec{a}}_{2},{\vec{b}}_{2})=\beta_{22}=3
.
And
\beta_{12}=[{\vec{a}}_{1}]_{|{\vec{a}}}^{T}.[\beta]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}.[{\vec{b}}_{2}]_{|{\vec{b}}}=\pmatrix{1&0}.\pmatrix{1&2\cr 0&3}.\pmatrix{0\cr 1}=2
.

Exercice A.35

Let
\beta\in{\cal L}(E,E;\mathbb{R})
, let
({\vec{a}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
be two bases in
A
, and let
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
. Prove:

\hbox{if, }\;\forall i\in[1,n]_{\mathbb{N}},\;\;{\vec{b}}_{i}=\lambda{\vec{a}}_{i},\quad\hbox{then}\quad[\beta]_{|{\vec{b}}}=\lambda^{2}[\beta]_{|{\vec{a}}}.

(A.43)

(A change of unit, e.g. from foot to metre, has a “big” influence on the matrix.)

Answer
.
{\vec{b}}_{i}=\lambda{\vec{a}}_{i}
give

\beta({\vec{b}}_{i},{\vec{b}}_{j})=\beta(\lambda{\vec{a}}_{i},\lambda{\vec{a}}_{j})=\lambda^{2}\beta({\vec{a}}_{i},{\vec{a}}_{j})
(bilinearity),
thus
[\beta]_{|{\vec{b}}}=\lambda^{2}[\beta]_{|{\vec{a}}}
.

Exercice A.36

Prove

[\beta^{T}]_{{\vec{b}},{\vec{a}}}=([\beta]_{{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}})^{T},\quad\hbox{written}\quad[\beta^{T}]=[\beta]^{T}.

(A.44)

Answer
. Let
[\beta]_{{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}=[\beta_{ij}]_{i=1,...,n\atop j=1,...,m}
and

[\beta^{T}]_{{\vec{b}},{\vec{a}}}=[\gamma_{ij}]_{i=1,...,m\atop j=1,...,n}
.
We have
\gamma_{ij}=\beta^{T}({\vec{b}}_{i},{\vec{a}}_{j})=\beta({\vec{a}}_{j},{\vec{b}}_{i})=\beta_{ji}
, qed.

A.8
Linear maps

A.8.1
Definition

Let
E
and
F
be vector spaces.

Definition A.37

\bullet
A function
L:E\rightarrow F
is linear iff

L({\vec{u}}_{1}+\lambda{\vec{u}}_{2})=L({\vec{u}}_{1})+\lambda L({\vec{u}}_{2})
for all
{\vec{u}}_{1},{\vec{u}}_{2}\in E
and all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}

(distributivity type relation).
And (distributivity notation):

L({\vec{u}}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}L.{\vec{u}},\quad\hbox{so}\quad L({\vec{u}}_{1}+\lambda{\vec{u}}_{2})=L.({\vec{u}}_{1}+\lambda{\vec{u}}_{2})=L.{\vec{u}}_{1}+\lambda L.{\vec{u}}_{2}.

(A.45)

NB: This dot notation
L.{\vec{u}}
is a linearity notation (distributivity type notation);
It is an “outer” dot product between a (linear) function and a vector;
It is
not
an “inner” dot product since
L
and
{\vec{u}}
don’t belong to a same space.
It is
not
a matrix product since no basis has been introduced yet (no quantification has been done yet).

\bullet

{\cal L}(E;F)
is the set of linear maps
E\rightarrow F
(vector space, subspace of
({\cal F}(E;F),+,.)
).

\bullet
If
F=E
then a linear map
L\in{\cal L}(E;E)
is called an endomorphism in
E
.

(If
F=\mathbb{R}
then a linear map
E\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is called a linear form, and
E^{*}:={\cal L}(E;\mathbb{R})
is the dual of
E
.)

Vocabulary:

Let
L_{i}(E;E)
be the space of linear invertible linear maps.
If
E
is a finite dimension vector space,
\dim E=n
,
then, in algebra, the set
(L_{i}(E;E),\circ)
of linear maps equipped with the composition rule is named
G\!L_{n}(E)
= “the linear group” (it is indeed a group, easy check). And the “linear group” of
n*n
invertible matrices is

G\!L_{n}({\cal M}_{\!n}):=(L_{i}({\cal M}_{\!n};{\cal M}_{\!n}),.)
, i.e.
L_{i}({\cal M}_{\!n};{\cal M}_{\!n})
with the matrix product rule.

Exercice A.38

(Math exercise.)
Let
E=(E,||.||_{E})
and
F=(F,||.||_{F})
be Banach spaces, and let
L_{ic}(E;F)
be the space of invertible linear continuous maps
E\rightarrow F
, with its usual norm
||L||=\sup_{||{\vec{x}}||_{E}=1}||L.{\vec{x}}||_{F}
.
Let

Z:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr L_{ic}(E;F)&\rightarrow L_{ic}(E;F)\cr L&\rightarrow L^{-1}\crcr}}\,\right\}
.
Prove
dZ(L).M=-L^{-1}\circ M\circ L^{-1}
, for all
M\in L_{ic}(E;F)
.
(Recall: In finite dimension, a linear map is always continuous.)

Answer
. Consider
\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{Z(L+hM)-Z(L)\over h}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{(L+hM)^{-1}-L^{-1}\over h}
(
{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}dZ(L).M
if the limit exists).
With
N=L^{-1}.M
we have

L+hM=L(I+hN)
, and
(I+hN)
is invertible as soon as
||hN||<1
, i.e.
h<{1\over||N||}={1\over||L^{-1}.M||}
,
its inverse being
I-hN+h^{2}N-...
(Neumann serie);
Thus
I+hN=I-hN+o(h)
,
and
(L+hM)^{-1}=(I+hN)^{-1}.L^{-1}=(I-hN+o(h)).L^{-1}=L^{-1}-hN.L^{-1}+o(h)
.
Thus

{(L+hM)^{-1}-L^{-1}\over h}={L^{-1}-hN.L^{-1}+o(h)-L^{-1}\over h}=-N.L^{-1}+o(1)\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{h\rightarrow 0}-N.L^{-1}
.

A.8.2
Quantification: Matrices
[L_{ij}]=[{L^{i}{}_{j}}]

\dim E=n
,
\dim F=m
,
L\in{\cal L}(E;F)
,

({\vec{a}}_{i})
is a basis in
E
which dual basis is
({\pi_{ai}})
,
({\vec{b}}_{i})
is a basis in
F
which dual basis is
({\pi_{bi}})
.
(With duality notations,
({\pi_{ai}})=(a^{i})
and
({\pi_{bi}})=(b^{i})
.)

Definition A.39

The components of a linear map
L\in{\cal L}(E;F)
relative to the bases
({\vec{a}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
are the
nm
reals named
L_{ij}
(classical notation)
={L^{i}{}_{j}}
(duality notation),
which are the components of the vectors
L.{\vec{a}}_{j}
relative to the basis
({\vec{b}}_{i})
. That is:

\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil& $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&\hbox{clas. not.}:&\;L.{\vec{a}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}L_{ij}{\vec{b}}_{i},\cr&\hbox{dual not.}&\;L.{\vec{a}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}{L^{i}{}_{j}}{\vec{b}}_{i},\cr}}\,\right\},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad[L.{\vec{a}}_{j}]_{|{\vec{b}}}\,\mathop{=}^{{\hbox{\footnotesize clas{.}}}}\pmatrix{L_{1j}\cr\vdots\cr L_{mj}}\mathop{=}^{{\hbox{\footnotesize dual}}}\pmatrix{L^{1}{}_{j}\cr\vdots\cr L^{m}{}_{j}}.

(A.46)

And

[L]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}\,\mathop{=}^{{\hbox{\footnotesize clas{.}}}}[L_{ij}]_{i=1,...,m\atop j=1,...,n}\mathop{=}^{{\hbox{\footnotesize dual}}}[{L^{i}{}_{j}}]_{i=1,...,m\atop j=1,...,n}

(A.47)

is the matrix of
L
relative to the bases
({\vec{a}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})

(so
[L.{\vec{a}}_{j}]_{|{\vec{b}}}
is the
j
-th column of
[L]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}
).

Particular case: If
E=F
(so
L
is an endomorphism) and if
({\vec{b}}_{i})=({\vec{a}}_{i})
then
[L]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{a}}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}[L]_{|{\vec{a}}}
.

Example A.40

n=m=2
.

[L]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}=\pmatrix{1&2\cr 0&3}

means
L.{\vec{a}}_{1}={\vec{b}}_{1}
and
L.{\vec{a}}_{2}=2{\vec{b}}_{1}+3{\vec{b}}_{2}
(column reading).
Here
L_{11}{=}1
,
L_{12}{=}2
,
L_{21}{=}0
,
L_{22}{=}3

(duality notations:
L^{1}{}_{1}{=}1
,
L^{1}{}_{2}{=}2
,
L^{2}{}_{1}{=}0
,
L^{2}{}_{2}{=}3
).

And
L
being linear, for all
{\vec{u}}\in E
,
{\vec{u}}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}u_{j}{\vec{a}}_{j}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}u^{j}{\vec{a}}_{j}
, we get, thanks to linearity,

L.{\vec{u}}\,\mathop{=}^{{\hbox{\footnotesize clas{.}}}}{\sum_{i=1}^{m}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}L_{ij}u_{j}{\vec{b}}_{i}\mathop{=}^{{\hbox{\footnotesize dual}}}{\sum_{i=1}^{m}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}{L^{i}{}_{j}}u^{j}{\vec{b}}_{i},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle[L.{\vec{u}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}=[L]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}.[{\vec{u}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}$}.

(A.48)

Shortened notation:
[L.{\vec{u}}]=[L].[{\vec{u}}]
when the bases are implicit.

Proposition A.41

A linear map
L\in{\cal L}(E;F)
is known as soon as the
n
vectors
L.{\vec{a}}_{j}
are known,
j\in[1,n]_{\mathbb{N}}
. And the linear maps
{\cal L}_{ij}\in{\cal L}(E;F)
defined by
{\cal L}_{ij}.{\vec{a}}_{\ell}=\delta_{j\ell}{\vec{b}}_{i}
(all the elements of the matrix
[{\cal L}_{ij}]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}
vanish except the element at the intersection of row
i
and column
j
which is equal to
1
), for
i,\ell=1,...,n
and
j=1,...,m
, constitute a basis
\in{\cal L}(E;F)
.
(Duality notations:
{\cal L}_{ij}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\cal L}_{i}{}^{j}
, and
{\cal L}_{i}{}^{j}.{\vec{a}}_{\ell}=\delta^{j}_{\ell}{\vec{b}}_{i}
.)
So,
\dim({\cal L}(E;F))=nm
.

Proof.
{\vec{u}}\in E
and
{\vec{u}}=\sum_{k}u_{j}{\vec{a}}_{j}
give
L.{\vec{u}}=\sum_{j}u_{j}L.{\vec{a}}_{j}
, since
L
is linear.
Thus
L
is known iff the
n
vectors
L.{\vec{a}}_{j}
are known for all
j=1,...,n
;
And
L.{\vec{a}}_{k}=\sum_{i}L_{ik}{\vec{b}}_{i}
together with

\sum_{ij}L_{ij}{\cal L}_{ij}.{\vec{a}}_{k}=\sum_{ij}L_{ij}\delta_{jk}{\vec{b}}_{i}=\sum_{i}L_{ik}{\vec{b}}_{i}
, for all
k
, thus
L=\sum_{ij}L_{ij}{\cal L}_{ij}
, thus the
{\cal L}_{ij}
span
{\cal L}(E;F)
.
And
{\sum_{i=1}^{m}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}\lambda_{ij}{\cal L}_{ij}=0
implies, for all
\ell
,

\vec{0}={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}\lambda_{ij}{\cal L}_{ij}.{\vec{a}}_{\ell}={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}\lambda_{ij}\delta_{j\ell}{\vec{b}}_{i}={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}\lambda_{i\ell}{\vec{b}}_{i}
, thus
\lambda_{i\ell}=0
, for all
i
and
\ell
.
Thus the
{\cal L}_{ij}
are independent.
Thus
({\cal L}_{ij})_{i=1,...,n\atop j=1,...,m}
is a basis in
{\cal L}(E;F)
.

Exercice A.42

If
L\in{\cal L}(E;E)
(endomorphism), if
({\vec{a}}_{i})
is a basis in
E
, prove:

\hbox{if }\;\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{*}\>\hbox{ and }\;{\vec{b}}_{i}=\lambda{\vec{a}}_{i}\>\;\forall i\in[1,n]_{\mathbb{N}},\quad\hbox{then}\quad[L]_{|{\vec{b}}}=[L]_{|{\vec{a}}},

(A.49)

i.e., a change of unit has not influence on the matrix of an endomorphism.
Check with the change of basis formulas.

NB:
To compare with (
A.43
): Covariance and contravariance should
not
be confused.

Answer
. Let
L.{\vec{a}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}L_{aij}{\vec{a}}_{i}

and
L.{\vec{b}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}L_{bij}{\vec{b}}_{i}
. Then

{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}L_{bij}{\vec{b}}_{i}=L.{\vec{b}}_{j}=L.(\lambda{\vec{a}}_{j})=\lambda L.{\vec{a}}_{j}=\lambda{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}L_{aij}{\vec{a}}_{i}=\lambda{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}L_{aij}{{\vec{b}}_{i}\over\lambda}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}L_{aij}{\vec{b}}_{i}
, thus
L_{bij}=L_{aij}
.

Change of basis formula:
[L]_{|{\vec{b}}}=P^{-1}.[L]_{|{\vec{a}}}.P
with
P=\lambda I
here.

A.8.3
Trace of an endomorphism

Let
E
be a vector space,
\dim E=n
. Let
{\vec{u}}\in E
and
\ell\in{E^{*}}
and call
{\cal L}_{{\vec{w}},\ell}\in{\cal L}(E;E)
the endomorphism, called an elementary endomorphism, defined by

{\cal L}_{{\vec{w}},\ell}.{\vec{u}}:={\vec{w}}(\ell.{\vec{u}})=(\ell.{\vec{u}}){\vec{w}}.

(A.50)

Definition A.43

The trace of the endomorphism
L_{{\vec{w}},\ell}
is the real

{\rm Tr}({\cal L}_{{\vec{w}},\ell}):=\ell.{\vec{w}}.

(A.51)

And the trace operator is the linear map

{\rm Tr}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\cal L}(E;E)&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr L&\rightarrow{\rm Tr}(L)\crcr}}\,\right\}
defined on elementary endomorphisms
\ell\otimes{\vec{w}}

by (
A.51
).

Proposition A.44

Let
L\in{\cal L}(E;E)
. The real
{\rm Tr}(L)
is objective (is intrinsic to
L
), i.e. is independent of any basis in
E
.
And (quantification) if
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is a basis and
L.{\vec{e}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}L_{ij}{\vec{e}}_{i}
for all
j
, then

{\rm Tr}(L)={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}L_{ii}\;(\in\mathbb{R}),

(A.52)

i.e.,
{\rm Tr}(L)
is the trace of the matrix
[L]_{|{\vec{e}}}
.
(Duality notations
L.{\vec{e}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{L^{i}{}_{j}}{\vec{e}}_{i}
and
{\rm Tr}(L)={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}L^{i}{}_{i}
.)

Proof.
{\rm Tr}({\cal L}_{{\vec{w}},\ell}):=\ell.{\vec{w}}
is a real that can be considered by any observer,
and which value is the same for all observers, cf. (
A.29
): It is objective.

Let
L\in{\cal L}(E;E)
.
Let
({\vec{a}}_{i})
be a basis and
({\pi_{ai}})
be its (covariant) dual basis,
and
L.{\vec{a}}_{j}=\sum_{i}L_{ij}{\vec{a}}_{i}
, i.e.
[L]_{[{\vec{a}}}=[L_{ij}]
.
And we have
(\sum_{ik}L_{ik}{\cal L}_{{\vec{a}}_{i},{\pi_{ak}}}).{\vec{a}}_{j}=\sum_{ik}L_{ik}{\cal L}_{{\vec{a}}_{i},{\pi_{ak}}}.{\vec{a}}_{j}\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqwol})}}\sum_{ik}L_{ik}{\vec{a}}_{i}({\pi_{ak}}.{\vec{a}}_{j})=\sum_{ik}L_{ik}{\vec{a}}_{i}\delta_{kj}=\sum_{i}L_{ij}{\vec{a}}_{i}
, thus
L=\sum_{ij}L_{ij}{\cal L}_{{\vec{a}}_{i},{\pi_{aj}}}
(sum of elementary endomorphisms), thus,
{\rm Tr}
being linear

{\rm Tr}(L)=\sum_{ij}L_{ij}{\rm Tr}{\cal L}_{{\vec{a}}_{i},{\pi_{aj}}}=\sum_{ij}L_{ij}\delta_{ji}=\sum_{i}L_{ii}
,
thus (
A.52
).

Exercice A.45

Check with the change of basis formula that
{\rm Tr}(L)
is an invariant (the same value for all observers).

Answer
. Let
({\vec{a}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
be two bases,
P=[P_{ij}]
be the transition matrix from
({\vec{a}}_{i})
to
({\vec{b}}_{i})
,
Q=P^{-1}
,
[L]_{[{\vec{a}}}=[(L_{a})_{ij}]
,
[L]_{[{\vec{b}}}=[(L_{b})_{ij}]
.
We have
[L]_{[{\vec{b}}}\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqLijoldnew2})}}P^{-1}.[L]_{[{\vec{a}}}.P
,
i.e.
(L_{b})_{ij}=\sum_{k\ell}Q_{ik}(L_{a})_{k\ell}P_{\ell j}
,
thus

\sum_{i}(L_{b})_{ii}=\sum_{ik\ell}Q_{ik}(L_{a})_{k\ell}P_{\ell i}=\sum_{k\ell}(P.Q)_{\ell k}(L_{a})_{k\ell}=\sum_{k\ell}\delta_{\ell k}(L_{a})_{k\ell}=\sum_{k}(L_{a})_{kk}
, qed.

Alternative definition with one-one tensors: see §
Q.6
.

A.9
Transposed matrix

The definition can be found in any elementary books, e.g., Strang
[
20
]
: If
M=[M_{ij}]_{i=1,...,m\atop j=1,...,n}
is an
m*n
matrix then its transposed is the
n*m
matrix

M^{T}=[(M^{T})_{ij}]_{i=1,...,n\atop j=1,...,m}
defined by

(M^{T})_{ij}:=M_{ji}

(A.53)

(exchange rows and columns).
E.g.,
M=\pmatrix{1&2\cr 3&4}
gives
M^{T}=\pmatrix{1&3\cr 2&4}
, and
(M^{T})_{12}{=}M_{21}{=}3
.

And
M
is symmetric iff
M^{T}=M
(this requires
m{=}n
).

And
M.N=[\sum_{k}M_{ik}N_{kj}]_{i\atop j}
gives

(M.N)^{T}=[\sum_{k}M_{jk}N_{ki}]_{i\atop j}=[\sum_{k}(N^{T})_{ik}(M^{T})_{kj}]_{i\atop j}=N^{T}.M^{T}
.

Exercice A.46

Prove: If
M
is an
n*n
invertible matrix then
M^{T}
is invertible and
(M^{T})^{-1}=(M^{-1})^{T}
(
{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}M^{-T}
); And if moreover
M
is symmetric, then
M^{-1}
is symmetric.

Answer
.
M.M^{-1}=I
gives
(M^{-1})^{T}.M^{T}=I^{T}=I
, thus
M^{T}
is invertible with
(M^{T})^{-1}=(M^{-1})^{T}
.
Moreover if
M=M^{T}
then
M^{-1}=(M^{-1})^{T}
.

A.10
A transposed endomorphism: depends on a chosen inner dot product

Not to be confused with the transposed of a matrix, cf. (
A.53
). And
not to be confused with the transposed of a bilinear form (observer independent), cf. (
A.33
);
In particular, a transposed of a linear map depends on the observer who use it (depends on the choice of an inner dot product).

A.10.1
Definition (requires an inner dot product: Not objective)

Let
E
be a finite dimensional vector space equipped with an inner dot product
