g(\cdot,\cdot)=(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
.

Definition A.47

The transpose of an endomorphism
L\in{\cal L}(E;E)
relative to
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}

is the endomorphism
L^{T}_{g}\in{\cal L}(E;E)
defined by

\forall{\vec{x}},{\vec{y}}\in E,\quad(L^{T}_{g}.{\vec{y}},{\vec{x}})_{g}=({\vec{y}},L.{\vec{x}})_{g},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad(L^{T}_{g}.{\vec{y}})\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,{\vec{x}}={\vec{y}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,(L.{\vec{x}}).

(A.54)

(It depends on
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
, see (
A.59
).)
If
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
is an imposed Euclidean dot product (isometric framework) then
L^{T}_{g}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}L^{T}
, thus
(L^{T}.{\vec{y}},{\vec{x}})_{g}=({\vec{y}},L.{\vec{x}})_{g}
,
i.e.
(L^{T}.{\vec{y}})\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{\vec{x}}={\vec{y}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}(L.{\vec{x}})
.

Exercice A.48

(Math exercise.)
The existence and uniqueness of
L_{g}^{T}
is e.g. proved with a basis in
E
when
E
is finite dimensional, see next §
A.10.2
.
More general proof: Prove: If
(E,(\cdot,\cdot)_{g})
is an infinite dimensional Hilbert space and if
L\in{\cal L}(E;E)
is continuous, then
L_{g}^{T}
exists, is unique, and is continuous (apply the Riesz representation theorem
F.1
).

Answer
. Let
{\vec{y}}\in E
, then let

\ell_{{\vec{y}}g}:{\vec{x}}\in E\rightarrow\ell_{{\vec{y}}g}({\vec{x}}):=({\vec{y}},L.{\vec{x}})_{g}\in\mathbb{R}
.
\ell_{{\vec{y}}g}
is linear (trivial since
L
is linear and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
is bilinear) and continuous:

|\ell_{{\vec{y}}g}.{\vec{x}}|\leq||{\vec{y}}||_{g}||L.{\vec{x}}||_{g}\leq||{\vec{y}}||_{g}||L||\,||{\vec{x}}||_{g}
gives
||\ell_{{\vec{y}}g}||_{E^{*}}\leq||L||\,||{\vec{y}}||_{g}<\infty
;
Let
{\vec{\ell}}_{{\vec{y}}g}\in E
be the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Riesz representation of
\ell_{{\vec{y}}g}\in E^{*}
:
\ell_{{\vec{y}}g}.{\vec{x}}=({\vec{\ell}}_{{\vec{y}}g},{\vec{x}})_{g}
for all
{\vec{x}}
, with
||{\vec{\ell}}_{{\vec{y}}g}||_{g}=||\ell_{{\vec{y}}g}||_{E^{*}}
;
We have thus defined
L^{T}_{g}:{\vec{y}}\in E\rightarrow L^{T}_{g}({\vec{y}}):={\vec{\ell}}_{{\vec{y}}g}\in E
;
with
(L^{T}_{g}({\vec{y}}),{\vec{x}})_{g}=({\vec{\ell}}_{{\vec{y}}g},{\vec{x}})_{g}=\ell_{{\vec{y}}g}.{\vec{x}}=({\vec{y}},L.{\vec{x}})_{g}
, thus
L^{T}_{g}
is linear
(since
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
is bilinear) and continuous:
||L^{T}_{g}.{\vec{y}}||_{g}=||{\vec{\ell}}_{{\vec{y}}g}||_{g}=||\ell_{{\vec{y}}g}||_{E^{*}}\leq||L||\,||{\vec{y}}||_{g}
gives
||L^{T}_{g}||\leq||L||_{{\cal L}(E;E)}<\infty
.

Remark A.49

Recall: The transposed
\beta^{T}
of a bilinear form
\beta
is objective, cf. (
A.33
): We don’t need any tool like an inner dot product to define
\beta^{T}
.
Not to be confused with: The transposed
L^{T}_{g}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}L^{T}
of a linear map
L
is subjective: It depends on a choice of an inner dot products
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
by an observer.
In particular it is
dangerous
to represent a linear map in a basis with its “bilinear tensorial representation” when dealing with the transposed:

L\in{\cal L}(E;F)
is naturally canonically represented by the bilinear form
\beta_{\!L}\in{\cal L}(F^{*},E;\mathbb{R})
, and thus

(\beta_{\!L})^{T}\in{\cal L}(E,F^{*};\mathbb{R})
; And

L.{\vec{a}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{L^{i}{}_{j}}{\vec{b}}_{i}\;\hbox{ gives }\;\beta_{\!L}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}{L^{i}{}_{j}}{\vec{b}}_{i}\otimes a^{j},\;\hbox{ thus }\;(\beta_{\!L})^{T}\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqbT})}{\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}{L^{j}{}_{i}}a^{i}\otimes{\vec{b}}_{j},

(A.55)

while
L^{T}\in{\cal L}(F;E)
is naturally canonically represented by the bilinear form

\beta_{(L^{T})}\in{\cal L}(E^{*},F;\mathbb{R})
, and

L^{T}.{\vec{b}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}(L^{T})^{i}{}_{j}{\vec{a}}_{i}\;\hbox{ gives }\;b_{(L^{T})}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}(L^{T})^{i}{}_{j}{\vec{a}}_{i}\otimes b^{j},\;\hbox{ thus }\;\framebox{$\displaystyle\beta_{(L^{T})}\neq(\beta_{\!L})^{T}$}

(A.56)

for two reasons: 1-
{\vec{a}}_{i}\otimes b^{j}\neq a^{i}\otimes{\vec{b}}_{j}
, and 2-

L^{T}:=L^{T}_{gh}
depends on chosen inner dot products
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
by observers in
E
and
F
, see (
A.73
):
(L^{T})^{i}{}_{j}\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqaltltb3})}{\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{n}}([g]^{-1})_{ik}L^{\ell}{}_{k}\,h_{\ell j}
;
While
(\beta_{\!L})^{T}
is independent of any inner dot products.
(In fact
(\beta_{\!L})^{T}\in{\cal L}(F^{*},E;\mathbb{R})
is the tensorial representation of the adjoint
L^{*}\in{\cal L}(F^{*};E^{*})
of
L
: With
L^{*}.b^{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}(L^{*})_{i}{}^{j}a^{i}
we get
\widetilde{(L^{*})}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}(L^{*})_{i}{}^{j}a^{i}\otimes{\vec{b}}_{j}=(\beta_{\!L})^{T}
,
see (
A.83
).)

So in continuum mechanics it is strongly advised
not
to use the tensorial notation for linear maps
when dealing with transposed (e.g. when using
F^{T}
the transposed of the deformation gradient).

A.10.2
Quantification with bases

Let
({\vec{e}}_{i})
be a basis in
E
, let
g_{ij}:=g({\vec{e}}_{i},{\vec{e}}_{j})
, so
[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}:=[g_{ij}]{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}[g]
, and let (classical notation)

L.{\vec{e}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}L_{ij}{\vec{e}}_{i},\quad L_{g}^{T}.{\vec{e}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}(L_{g}^{T})_{ij},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad[L]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[L_{ij}]{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}[L],\quad[L_{g}^{T}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[(L_{g}^{T})_{ij}]{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}[L_{g}^{T}].

(A.57)

(
A.54
) gives

\displaystyle[{\vec{x}}]^{T}.[g].[L^{T}_{g}.{\vec{y}}]=[L.{\vec{x}}]^{T}.[g].[{\vec{y}}]
for all
{\vec{x}},{\vec{y}}
,
thus

[g].[L^{T}_{g}]=[L]^{T}.[g],\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad{\sum_{k=1}^{n}}g_{ik}(L_{g}^{T})_{kj}={\sum_{k=1}^{n}}L_{ki}\,g_{kj}

(A.58)

i.e.,

\framebox{$\displaystyle[L^{T}_{g}]=[g]^{-1}.[L]^{T}.[g]$},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad(L_{g}^{T})_{ij}={\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{n}}([g]^{-1})_{ik}L_{\ell k}g_{\ell j}.

(A.59)

To compare with (
A.56
).
If and only if
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-orthonormal then
[g]=[\delta_{ij}]
and
(L_{g}^{T})_{ij}=L_{ji}
.
With duality notations,

L.{\vec{e}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}L^{i}{}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i},\;L_{g}^{T}.{\vec{e}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}(L_{g}^{T})^{i}{}_{j},\;[L]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[L^{i}{}_{j}],\;[L_{g}^{T}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[(L_{g}^{T})^{i}{}_{j}],

and

{\sum_{k=1}^{n}}g_{ik}(L_{g}^{T})^{k}{}_{j}={\sum_{k=1}^{n}}L^{k}{}_{i}\,g_{kj},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad(L_{g}^{T})^{i}{}_{j}={\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{n}}([g]^{-1})_{ik}L^{\ell}{}_{k}\,g_{\ell j}.

(A.60)

Remark A.50

The last equation (
A.60
)
2
is also written

(L_{g}^{T})^{i}{}_{j}={\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{n}}g^{ik}L^{\ell}{}_{k}\,g_{\ell j}\quad\hbox{when}\quad([g]_{{\vec{e}}})^{-1}=[g_{ij}]^{-1}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}[g^{ij}].

(A.61)

Don’t be fooled by the notation
g^{ij}
, defined by
[g^{ij}]:=[g_{ij}]^{-1}
.
(It is also the short notation for
(g^{\sharp})^{ij}
, see (
F.32
).)
Use classical notations to avoid misuses and misinterpretations.

Remark A.51

A bilinear form
\beta\in{\cal L}(E,E;\mathbb{R})
satisfies
[\beta^{T}]=[\beta]^{T}
.
A linear endomorphism
L\in{\cal L}(E;E)
satisfies
[L^{T}_{g}]=[g]^{-1}.[L]^{T}.[g]\neq[L]^{T}
in general
(e.g. take
[L]=\pmatrix{0&1\cr 1&0}
and
[g]=\pmatrix{1&0\cr 0&2}
).
So do not confuse a bilinear on
E
(objective) with a linear endomorphism on
E
(subjective).

Exercice A.52

In
{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{2}}}
, let
({\vec{e}}_{1},{\vec{e}}_{2})
be a basis.
Let
L\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}^{2}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{2}}})
be defined by
[L]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{0&1\cr 1&0}
.
Find two inner dot products
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{2}}}
such that
L^{T}_{g}\neq L^{T}_{h}
(a transposed endomorphism is not unique, is not intrinsic to
L
, since it depends on a choice of an inner dot product by an observer).

Answer
. Calculations with (
A.58
):

Choose
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
given by
[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{1&0\cr 0&1}=[I]
. Thus

[L_{g}^{T}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[I].[L]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[I]=\pmatrix{0&1\cr 1&0}
; So
L_{g}^{T}=L
.

Choose
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
given by
[h]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{1&0\cr 0&2}
. Thus

[L_{h}^{T}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[h]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{-1}.[L]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[h]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{0&2\cr{1\over 2}&0}
; So
L_{h}^{T}\neq L
.

Thus
L_{h}^{T}\neq L_{g}^{T}
, e.g.,
{\vec{e}}_{2}=L_{g}^{T}.{\vec{e}}_{1}\neq L_{h}^{T}.{\vec{e}}_{1}={1\over 2}{\vec{e}}_{2}
.

Exercice A.53

Prove: If
L
is invertible then
L_{g}^{T}
is invertible, and
(L_{g}^{T})^{-1}=(L^{-1})_{g}^{T}
(written
L_{g}^{-T}
).

Answer
. Suppose:
\exists{\vec{y}}\in E
,
{\vec{y}}\neq\vec{0}
, s.t.
L_{g}^{T}.{\vec{y}}=0
.

L
being invertible,
\exists!{\vec{x}}\in E
s.t.
L.{\vec{x}}={\vec{y}}
, with
{\vec{x}}\neq\vec{0}
since
{\vec{y}}\neq\vec{0}

and
L
is linear; And
L_{g}^{T}.{\vec{y}}=0
gives
L_{g}^{T}.L.{\vec{x}}=0
, thus
(L_{g}^{T}.L.{\vec{x}},{\vec{x}})_{g}=0
, thus
||L.{\vec{x}}||_{g}^{2}=0
,
thus
L.{\vec{x}}=0
, thus
{\vec{x}}=0
since
L
is linear bijective; Absurd. Thus
{\rm Ker}(L_{g}^{T})=\{\vec{0}\}
, thus
L_{g}^{T}
is invertible since it is an endomorphism.
And

\displaystyle(L_{g}^{T}.(L^{-1})^{T}_{g}.{\vec{x}},{\vec{y}})_{g}\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqseccpgdd0e})}((L^{-1})^{T}_{g}.{\vec{x}},L.{\vec{y}})_{g}\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqseccpgdd0e})}({\vec{x}},(L^{-1}).L.{\vec{y}})_{g}=({\vec{x}},{\vec{y}})_{g}=(L_{g}^{T}.(L_{g}^{T})^{-1}.{\vec{x}},{\vec{y}})_{g}
,
true
\forall{\vec{x}},{\vec{y}}
, thus
L_{g}^{T}.(L^{-1})^{T}_{g}=L_{g}^{T}.(L_{g}^{T})^{-1}
, thus
(L^{-1})^{T}_{g}=(L_{g}^{T})^{-1}

since
L_{g}^{T}
is invertible.

Exercice A.54

Special case of proportional inner dot products
(\cdot,\cdot)_{a}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{b}
:

\exists\lambda>0
s.t.
(\cdot,\cdot)_{a}=\lambda^{2}(\cdot,\cdot)_{b}
.
Prove:
L_{a}^{T}=L_{b}^{T}
:
Two proportional inner dot products give the same transposed endomorphism.

Answer
.
(L^{T}_{b}.{\vec{y}},{\vec{x}})_{b}=({\vec{y}},L.{\vec{x}})_{b}=\lambda^{2}({\vec{y}},L.{\vec{x}})_{a}=\lambda^{2}(L^{T}_{a}.{\vec{y}},{\vec{x}})_{a}=(L^{T}_{a}.{\vec{y}},{\vec{x}})_{b}
, for all
{\vec{x}},{\vec{y}}
, so
L^{T}_{b}=L^{T}_{a}
.

A.10.3
Symmetric endomorphism

Definition A.55

An endomorphism
L\in{\cal L}(E;E)
is
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-symmetric iff

L^{T}_{g}=L
:

\hbox{$L$ $(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}$-symmetric}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad L^{T}_{g}=L\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad(L.{\vec{x}},{\vec{y}})_{g}=({\vec{x}},L.{\vec{y}})_{g},\quad\forall{\vec{x}},{\vec{y}}\in E.

(A.62)

Remark A.56

The symmetric character of an endomorphism
L
is not intrinsic to the endomorphism:
It depends on
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
;
See exercise
A.52
where

L
is
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-symmetric while it is
not

(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
-symmetric.

A.10.4
The general flat
♭
notation for an endomorphism: Relative to a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}

Let
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
be an inner dot product in a vector space
E
, and let
L\in{\cal L}(E;E)
(a
C^{0}
endomorphism).

Definition A.57

The bilinear form
L^{\flat}_{g}\in{\cal L}(E,E;\mathbb{R})
which is
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-associated to the endomorphism
L\in{\cal L}(E;E)
is defined by, for all
{\vec{u}},{\vec{w}}\in E
,

L^{\flat}_{g}({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}}):=({\vec{u}},L.{\vec{w}})_{g}.

(A.63)

(
L^{\flat}_{g}
depends on a choice of a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
.)
We have thus defined the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-dependent operator:

(.)^{\flat}_{g}={\cal J}_{g}(.):\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\cal L}(E;E)&\rightarrow{\cal L}(E,E;\mathbb{R})\cr L&\rightarrow{\cal J}_{g}(L):=L^{\flat}_{g},\crcr}}\,\right.

(A.64)

If
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
is imposed, then
L^{\flat}_{g}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}L^{\flat}
.

(The bilinearity of
L^{\flat}_{g}
is trivial since
L
is linear and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
is bilinear, and the bilinear form
L^{\flat}_{g}
continuous as soon as
L
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
are since

|L^{\flat}_{g}({\vec{u}},{\vec{v}})|\leq||g||\,||L.{\vec{u}}||\,||{\vec{v}}||\leq(||g||\,||L||)\,||{\vec{u}}||\,||{\vec{v}}||
.)

Proposition A.58

With the natural canonical isomorphism
L\in{\cal L}(E;E)\simeq T_{L}\in{\cal L}(E^{*},E;\mathbb{R})
given by

T_{L}(\ell,{\vec{w}})=\ell.L.{\vec{w}}
, and with
T_{L}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}L
, the function
(.)^{\flat}_{g}
is the change of contravariance to covariance mapping given by

L^{\flat}_{g}=g.L.

(A.65)

Proof.
Recall: The contraction of an elementary
{0\choose 2}
tensor
\ell_{1}\otimes\ell_{2}
with an elementary
{1\choose 1}
tensor
{\vec{v}}\otimes\ell_{3}
is the
{0\choose 2}
tensor

(\ell_{1}\otimes\ell_{2}).({\vec{v}}\otimes\ell_{3}):=(\ell_{2}.{\vec{v}})\ell_{1}\otimes\ell_{3}
. And the contraction on any tensors is the bilinear map defined on elementaty tensors.
So, with a basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
in
E
and its dual basis
(e^{i})
in
{E^{*}}
,
if
g=\sum_{ij}g_{ij}e^{i}\otimes e^{j}
and
L=\sum_{ij}{L^{i}{}_{j}}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
then

g.L=\sum_{ijk}g_{ik}L^{k}{}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
.
Thus
(g.L)({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})=\sum_{ij}u^{i}w^{j}(g.L)({\vec{e}}_{i},{\vec{e}}_{j})=\sum_{ijk}u^{i}w^{j}g_{ik}L^{k}{}_{j}=\sum_{ik}u^{i}g_{ik}(L.{\vec{w}})^{k}=\sum_{ik}u^{i}g_{ik}(L.{\vec{w}})^{k}=g({\vec{u}},L.{\vec{w}})=L^{\flat}_{g}({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})
.

Quantification:

Let
({\vec{e}}_{i})
be a basis in
E
, and, with duality notations motivated by the flat notation
“
i
top changed into
i
bottom” in the components
{L^{i}{}_{j}}
of
L
, let
g_{ij}:=g({\vec{e}}_{i},{\vec{e}}_{j})
,
L.{\vec{e}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{L^{i}{}_{j}}{\vec{e}}_{i}
and
L^{\flat}_{g,ij}=L^{\flat}_{g}({\vec{e}}_{i},{\vec{e}}_{j})
,
i.e. with tensorial notations for calculations

g=\sum_{ij}g_{ij}e^{i}\otimes e^{j},\quad L=\sum_{ij}{L^{i}{}_{j}}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j},\quad L^{\flat}_{g}=\sum_{ij}L^{\flat}_{g,ij}e^{i}\otimes e^{j}.

(A.66)

So
[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[g_{ij}]
,
[L]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[{L^{i}{}_{j}}]
and
[L^{\flat}_{g}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[L^{\flat}_{g,ij}]
.
Then (
A.65
) gives (or see next exercise)

\framebox{$\displaystyle[L^{\flat}_{g}]=[g].[L]$}.

(A.67)

Exercice A.59

Prove (
A.67
) with components.

Answer
. With (
A.63
) we get

\displaystyle L^{\flat}_{g,ij}=L^{\flat}_{g}({\vec{e}}_{i},{\vec{e}}_{j})=({\vec{e}}_{i},L.{\vec{e}}_{j})_{g}=({\vec{e}}_{i},\sum_{k}L^{k}{}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{k})_{g}=\sum_{k}L^{k}{}_{j}g_{ik}=([g].[L])_{ij}
.

Remark A.60

A change of variance, here from the
{1\choose 1}
type tensor
L
to the
{0\choose 2}
tensor
L^{\flat}_{g}
,
is necessarily observer dependent:
There is no natural canonical isomorphism between a vector space
E
and its dual
E^{*}
, see §
T.2
.
Details: Here fix
{\vec{w}}
and write
\ell_{g,{\vec{w}}}({\vec{u}})=({\vec{u}},L.{\vec{w}})_{g}
(
=L^{\flat}_{g}({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})
);
Thus
\ell_{g,{\vec{w}}}\in E^{*}
is the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-representation function (linear form) of the vector
L.{\vec{w}}
,
i.e.
\ell_{g,{\vec{w}}}={\vec{R}}_{g}(L.{\vec{w}})
where
{\vec{R}}_{g}
is the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Riesz-representation operator (the change of variance operator, see (
F.3
).

A.11
A transposed of a linear map: depends on chosen inner dot products

This paragraph is needed to define the transposed of the deformation gradient.

Not to be confused with the transposed of a matrix, cf. (
A.53
). And
not to be confused with the objective transposed of a bilinear form, cf. (
A.33
);
E.g., a transposed of a linear map is
not
objective.

A.11.1
Definition (subjective)

(E,(\cdot,\cdot)_{g})
and
(F,(\cdot,\cdot)_{h})
are Hilbert spaces, and
L\in{\cal L}(E;F)

(which is supposed to be continuous if
E
and
F
are infinite dimensional).
E.g.,
E={\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
,
F={\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
,
L=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})=
the deformation gradient, cf. (
4.1
),

(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
is the foot built Euclidean dot product chosen by the observer who made the measurements at
{t_{0}}
,

(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
is the metre built Euclidean dot product chosen by the observer who makes the measurements at
t
.

Definition A.61

The transposed of
L\in{\cal L}(E;F)
relative to
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}

is the linear map
L^{T}_{gh}\in{\cal L}(F;E)
defined by, for all
({\vec{x}},{\vec{y}})\in E\times F
,

(L^{T}_{gh}.{\vec{y}},{\vec{x}})_{g}=({\vec{y}},L.{\vec{x}})_{h},

(A.68)

where we used the dot notation
L^{T}_{gh}({\vec{y}}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}L^{T}_{gh}.{\vec{y}}
since
L^{T}_{gh}
is linear.
This defines the map

(.)^{T}_{gh}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\cal L}(E;F)&\rightarrow{\cal L}(F;E)\cr L&\rightarrow(.)^{T}_{gh}(L):=L^{T}_{gh}\crcr}}\,\right.

(A.69)

NB: So a linear map has an infinite number of transposed (it depends on inner dot products).

Notation: If
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
are imposed then
L^{T}_{gh}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}L^{T}
.

And if
F=E
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}=(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
then
L^{T}_{gh}=L^{T}_{g}
, see §
A.10
.

A.11.2
Quantification with bases

Let
({\vec{a}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
be bases in
E
and
F
,
let
g_{ij}:=g({\vec{a}}_{i},{\vec{a}}_{j})
,
h_{ij}:=h({\vec{b}}_{i},{\vec{b}}_{j})
,
[g]_{|{\vec{a}}}=[g_{ij}]
,
[h]_{|{\vec{b}}}=[h_{ij}]
, and let (classical notation)

\displaystyle{{}L.{\vec{a}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}L_{ij}{\vec{b}}_{i},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad[L]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}=[L_{ij}]{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}[L],}

\displaystyle{{}L_{gh}^{T}.{\vec{b}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}(L_{gh}^{T})_{ij}{\vec{a}}_{i},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad[L_{gh}^{T}]_{|{\vec{b}},{\vec{a}}}=[(L_{gh}^{T})_{ij}]{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}[L_{gh}^{T}].}

(A.70)

(
A.68
) gives

[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}^{T}.[g]_{|{\vec{a}}}.[L_{gh}^{T}.{\vec{y}}]_{|{\vec{y}}}=([L.{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{b}}})^{T}.[h]_{|{\vec{b}}}.[{\vec{y}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}

for all
{\vec{x}},{\vec{y}}
, thus,

[g]_{|{\vec{a}}}.[L_{gh}^{T}]_{|{\vec{b}},{\vec{a}}}=([L]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}})^{T}.[h]_{|{\vec{b}}}
and

[L_{gh}^{T}]_{|{\vec{b}},{\vec{a}}}=[g]_{|{\vec{a}}}^{-1}.([L]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}})^{T}.[h]_{|{\vec{b}}}
.
Shortened notation:

[g].[L^{T}]=[L]^{T}.[h],\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad{\sum_{k=1}^{n}}g_{ik}(L_{gh}^{T})_{kj}={\sum_{k=1}^{m}}L_{ki}\,h_{kj},

(A.71)

i.e.

\framebox{$\displaystyle[L^{T}]=[g]^{-1}.[L]^{T}.[h]$},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad(L_{gh}^{T})_{ij}={\sum_{k=1}^{n}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}}([g]^{-1})_{ik}L_{\ell k}h_{\ell j}.

(A.72)

With duality notations,

L.{\vec{e}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}L^{i}{}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i},\;[L]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[L^{i}{}_{j}],\;L_{gh}^{T}.{\vec{e}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}(L_{gh}^{T})^{i}{}_{j},\;[L_{gh}^{T}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[(L_{gh}^{T})^{i}{}_{j}],

and

{\sum_{k=1}^{n}}g_{ik}(L_{gh}^{T})^{k}{}_{j}={\sum_{k=1}^{n}}L^{k}{}_{i}\,h_{kj},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad(L_{gh}^{T})^{i}{}_{j}={\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{n}}([g]^{-1})_{ik}L^{\ell}{}_{k}\,h_{\ell j}\quad({\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{n}}(g^{ik}L^{\ell}{}_{k}\,h_{\ell j}).

(A.73)

(Be careful with the notation
([g]^{-1})_{ik}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}g^{ij}
, see remark
A.50
.)

Exercice A.62

Prove: If
L
is invertible then
(L_{gh}^{T})^{-1}=(L^{-1})^{T}_{hg}
.

Answer
.
(L_{gh}^{T}.(L^{-1})^{T}_{hg}.{\vec{x}},{\vec{y}})_{g}=((L^{-1})^{T}_{hg}.{\vec{x}},L.{\vec{y}})_{h}=({\vec{x}},L^{-1}.L.{\vec{y}})_{g}=({\vec{x}},{\vec{y}})_{g}=(L_{gh}^{T}.(L_{gh}^{T})^{-1}.{\vec{x}},{\vec{y}})_{g}
,
true
\forall{\vec{x}},{\vec{y}}
.

A.11.3
Deformation gradient symmetric: Absurd

The symmetry of a linear map
L\in{\cal L}(E;F)
is a nonsense if
E\neq F
.

E.g.: The gradient of deformation
{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}F\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})
cannot be symmetric since
F^{T}\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}})
.
Idem for the first Piola–Kirchhoff tensor
{P\!\!\!K^{t_{0}}_{t}}
, which motivates
the introduction of the symmetric second Piola–Kirchhoff tensor

{S\!\!K^{t_{0}}_{t}}
, see Marsden–Hughes
[
14
]
or §
M.2.3
.

A.11.4
Isometry

Definition A.63

A linear map
L\in{\cal L}(E;F)
is an isometry relative to
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
iff

\forall{\vec{x}},{\vec{y}}\in E,\quad(L.{\vec{x}},L.{\vec{y}})_{h}=({\vec{x}},{\vec{y}})_{g},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad L^{T}_{gh}\circ L=I_{E}\hbox{ (identity in $E$)}.

(A.74)

In particular, an endomorphism
L\in{\cal L}(E;E)
is a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-isometry iff

\forall{\vec{x}},{\vec{y}}\in E,\quad(L.{\vec{x}},L.{\vec{y}})_{g}=({\vec{x}},{\vec{y}})_{g},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad L^{T}_{g}\circ L=I_{E}.

(A.75)

Thus, if
L\in{\cal L}(E;F)
is an isometry and
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-orthonormal basis, then
(L.{\vec{e}}_{i})
is a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
-orthonormal basis, since
(L.{\vec{e}}_{i},L.{\vec{e}}_{j})_{h}=({\vec{e}}_{i},{\vec{e}}_{j})_{g}=\delta_{ij}
for all
i,j
.

Exercice A.64

Let
{\vec{f}}:E\rightarrow F
.
Prove:

\hbox{if \ ${\vec{f}}$ is an isometry}\;\;\hbox{then}\;\;\hbox{${\vec{f}}$ is linear}.

(A.76)

Answer
. Let
({\vec{e}}_{i})
be a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-orthonormal basis; Thus
({\vec{f}}({\vec{e}}_{i}))
is a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
-orthonormal basis (since
{\vec{f}}
is an isometry).
Thus, if
{\vec{x}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
then

\displaystyle{\vec{f}}({\vec{x}})\mathop{=}^{b.o.n.}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}({\vec{f}}({\vec{x}}),{\vec{f}}({\vec{e}}_{i}))_{h}{\vec{f}}({\vec{e}}_{i})\mathop{=}^{hyp.}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}({\vec{x}},{\vec{e}}_{i})_{g}{\vec{f}}({\vec{e}}_{i})\mathop{=}^{b.o.n.}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x_{i}{\vec{f}}({\vec{e}}_{i})
,
thus
\displaystyle{\vec{f}}({\vec{x}}+\lambda{\vec{y}})={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}(x_{i}+\lambda y_{i}){\vec{f}}({\vec{e}}_{i})={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x_{i}{\vec{f}}({\vec{e}}_{i})+\lambda{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}y_{i}{\vec{f}}({\vec{e}}_{i})={\vec{f}}({\vec{x}})+\lambda{\vec{f}}({\vec{y}})
,
thus
{\vec{f}}
is linear.

Exercice A.65

{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
is an affine space,
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
is the usual associated vector space, and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
is an inner dot product in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
. Definition: A distance-preserving function
f:p\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rightarrow f(p)\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
is a function s.t.

||\overrightarrow{f(p)f(q)}||_{g}=||\overrightarrow{pq}||_{g},\quad\forall p,q\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}.

(A.77)

Prove: If
f
is a distance-preserving function, then
f
is affine.

Answer
. Let
O\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
(an origin)
and
{\vec{f}}:{\vec{x}}=\overrightarrow{Op}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\rightarrow{\vec{f}}({\vec{x}}):=\overrightarrow{f(O)f(p)}

(vectorial associated function).
Let
{\vec{x}}=\overrightarrow{Op}
and
{\vec{y}}=\overrightarrow{Oq}
.
Then the remarkable identity

\displaystyle 2({\vec{f}}({\vec{x}}),{\vec{f}}({\vec{y}}))_{g}=||{\vec{f}}({\vec{x}})||^{2}_{g}+||{\vec{f}}({\vec{y}})||^{2}_{g}-||{\vec{f}}({\vec{x}}){-}{\vec{f}}({\vec{y}})||^{2}_{g}
gives

\displaystyle 2({\vec{f}}({\vec{x}}),{\vec{f}}({\vec{y}}))_{g}=||{\vec{f}}({\vec{x}})||^{2}_{g}+||{\vec{f}}({\vec{y}})||^{2}_{g}-||\overrightarrow{f(q)f(p)}||^{2}_{g}=||{\vec{f}}({\vec{x}})||^{2}_{g}+||{\vec{f}}({\vec{y}})||^{2}_{g}-||\overrightarrow{qp}||^{2}_{g}=||{\vec{x}}||^{2}_{g}+||{\vec{y}}||^{2}_{g}-||{\vec{x}}-{\vec{y}}||^{2}_{g}=2({\vec{x}},{\vec{y}})_{g}
, thus
{\vec{f}}
is an isometry, thus
{\vec{f}}
is linear cf. (
A.76
),
thus
f
is affine since

f(p)=f(O)+{\vec{f}}(\overrightarrow{Op})
.

A.12
The adjoint of a linear map (objective)

(For mathematicians; May produce misunderstandings, misuses, problematic mechanical interpretations.)

No inner dot product is required here: A linear map
L
has only one adjoint
L^{*}
(intrinsic to
L
);
While
L
has many transposed
L^{T}=L^{T}_{gh}
which depend on inner dot products.

A.12.1
Definition

E
and
F
are vector spaces, and
E^{*}={\cal L}(E;\mathbb{R})
and
F^{*}={\cal L}(F;\mathbb{R})
are the dual spaces (made of linear continuous forms). (If
E
and
F
are finite dimensional, the continuity is always satisfied.)

Definition A.66

Let
L\in{\cal L}(E;F)
(linear and continuous); Its adjoint is the linear map
L^{*}\in{\cal L}(F^{*};E^{*})
canonically defined by

L^{*}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr F^{*}&\rightarrow E^{*}\cr m&\rightarrow L^{*}(m):=m\circ L,\crcr}}\,\right.

(A.78)

i.e., for all
({\vec{x}},m)\in E\times F^{*}
,

(L^{*}(m))({\vec{x}}):=m(L({\vec{x}})).

(A.79)

(The adjoint
L^{*}
cannot be confused with a transposed
L^{T}
which requires inner dot products, cf. (
A.68
).)

The linearity of
L^{*}
is trivial, thus, together with the linearity of
m
and
L
, we can use the dot notation:

L^{*}.m:=m.L,\quad\hbox{and}\quad(L^{*}.m).{\vec{x}}:=m.L.{\vec{x}}.

(A.80)

And
||L^{*}.m||_{E^{*}}=||m.L||_{E^{*}}\leq||m||_{F^{*}}||L||_{{\cal L}(E;F)}
gives
||L^{*}||_{{\cal L}(F^{*};E^{*})}\leq||L||_{{\cal L}(E;F)}<\infty
, thus
L^{*}
is continuous (when
L
is).

A.12.2
Quantification

E
and
F
are finite dimensional,
\dim E=n
,
\dim F=m
, and
({\vec{a}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
are bases in
E
and
F
.
Let
[L]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}[L]
,
[L^{*}]_{|b,a}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}[L^{*}]
,
[m]_{|b}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}[m]
and
[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}[{\vec{x}}]
be the matrices relative to the chosen bases:
(
A.80
) gives

([L^{*}].[m].[{\vec{x}}]=[m].[L].[{\vec{x}}]
for all
{\vec{x}}\in E
and
m\in{F^{*}}
, thus, for all
m\in F^{*}
(recall that
[m]
is a line matrix),
thus
[L^{*}].[m]^{T}=([L]^{T}.[m]^{T}
,
thus

\framebox{$\displaystyle[L^{*}]=[L]^{T}$}\quad(\hbox{transposed matrix}).

(A.81)

(Full notation:
[L^{*}]_{|b,a}=([L]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}})^{T}
.)

Details:
With the dual bases
(\pi_{ai})
and
(\pi_{bi})
,
with
L.{\vec{a}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}L_{ij}{\vec{b}}_{i}
, i.e.
[L]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}=[L_{ij}]_{i=1,...,m\hfill\atop j=1,...,n}
,
and with

L^{*}.\pi_{bj}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}(L^{*})_{ij}\pi_{ai}
, i.e.
[L^{*}]_{|b,a}=[(L^{*})_{ij}]_{i=1,...,n\hfill\atop j=1,...,m}
,
(
A.80
) gives, for all
(i,j)\in[1,n]_{\mathbb{N}}\times[1,m]_{\mathbb{N}}
,

(L^{*}.\pi_{bj}).{\vec{a}}_{i}=\pi_{bj}.(L.{\vec{a}}_{i}),\quad\hbox{thus}\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle(L^{*})_{ij}=L_{ji}$}\quad\hbox{and}\quad[L^{*}]=[L]^{T}.

(A.82)

Duality notations (warning: can be misused):
L.{\vec{a}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}{L^{i}{}_{j}}{\vec{b}}_{i}
, i.e.
[L]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}=[{L^{i}{}_{j}}]_{i=1,...,m\hfill\atop j=1,...,n}
, and

L^{*}.b^{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}(L^{*})_{i}\,^{j}a^{i}
, i.e.
[L^{*}]_{|b,a}=[((L^{*})_{i}\,^{j}]_{i=1,...,n\hfill\atop j=1,...,m}
, thus, for all
(i,j)\in[1,n]_{\mathbb{N}}\times[1,m]_{\mathbb{N}}
,

(L^{*}.b^{j}).{\vec{a}}_{i}=b^{j}.(L.{\vec{a}}_{i}),\quad\hbox{thus}\quad(L^{*})_{i}\,^{j}=L^{j}{}_{i}\quad\hbox{and}\quad[L^{*}]=[L]^{T}.

(A.83)

(Recall: Use classical notations if in doubt, or, preferably, don’t use duality notations here.)

Remark A.67

Reminder: The transposed
b^{T}
of a bilinear
b
form is intrinsic to
b
, and the adjoint
L^{*}
of a linear map
L
is intrinsic to
L
; But a transposed
L^{T}
of a linear form
L
is
not
intrinsic to the linear form (it depends on chosen inner dot products): Watch out for the (unfortunate) vocabulary “transpose”!

A.12.3
Relation with the transposed when inner dot products are introduced

let
L\in{\cal L}(E;F)
. We need inner dot products
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
in
E
and
F
to define
L^{T}=L^{T}_{gh}
. To have a functional relation between
L^{*}
and
L^{T}_{gh}
, we use
the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Riesz representation mapping

{\vec{R}}_{g}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr E^{*}&\rightarrow E\cr\ell&\rightarrow{\vec{R}}_{g}(\ell)={\vec{\ell}}_{g}\crcr}}\,\right\}
,
where
\ell.{\vec{x}}=({\vec{\ell}}_{g},{\vec{x}})_{g}
for all
{\vec{x}}\in E
, see (
F.3
); idem with
F
.

Let
L\in{\cal L}(E;F)
(continuous).
For all
{\vec{x}}\in E
and all
m\in{F^{*}}
we have

(L^{*}.m).{\vec{x}}\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdefadjL0b})}m.(L.{\vec{x}}),\quad\hbox{thus}\quad({\vec{R}}_{g}(L^{*}.m),{\vec{x}})_{g}=({\vec{R}}_{h}(m),L.{\vec{x}})_{h},

(A.84)

thus
(({\vec{R}}_{g}\circ L^{*}).m),{\vec{x}})_{g}=((L^{T}_{gh}\circ{\vec{R}}_{h}).m,L.{\vec{x}})_{g}
.
Thus
{\vec{R}}_{g}\circ L^{*}=L^{T}_{gh}\circ{\vec{R}}_{h}
, i.e.

\framebox{$\displaystyle L^{T}_{gh}={\vec{R}}_{g}\circ L^{*}\circ({\vec{R}}_{h})^{-1}$}\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr E\;\mathop{\longleftarrow}^{\displaystyle\;\;L^{T}_{gh}}\;&F\cr\vskip-4.0pt\cr{\vec{R}}_{g}\uparrow\qquad\quad&\;\;\uparrow{\vec{R}}_{h}\cr\vskip-2.0pt\cr E^{*}\;\mathop{\longleftarrow}_{\displaystyle\;L^{*}}\;&F^{*}\cr}}\,\;\hbox{ is a commutative diagram}.

(A.85)

Exercice A.68

From (
A.85
),
recover (
A.71
), i.e.
[L_{gh}^{T}]=[g]^{-1}.[L]^{T}.[h]
.

Answer
.
[L^{T}_{gh}]\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{defadjLqR})}}[{\vec{R}}_{g}].[L^{*}].[{\vec{R}}_{h}]^{-1}\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqRieszbase})}}[g]^{-1}.[L]^{T}.[h]
.

A.13
Tensorial representation of a linear map

A.13.1
A tensorial representation

Consider the natural canonical isomorphism (between linear maps
E\rightarrow F
and bilinear forms
F^{*}\times E\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
)

{\widetilde{{\cal J}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\cal L}(E;F)&\rightarrow{\cal L}(F^{*},E;\mathbb{R})\cr L&\rightarrow\beta_{\!L}={\widetilde{{\cal J}}}(L)\crcr}}\,\right\}\quad\hbox{where}\quad\beta_{\!L}(m,{\vec{u}}):=m.(L.{\vec{u}}),\quad\forall(m,{\vec{u}})\in F^{*}\times E,

(A.86)

see §
T.4
. And
\beta_{\!L}
is also named
L
for calculations purposes, see (
A.89
).

(NB: It can be dangerous to substitute
L
with
\beta_{\!L}
, see e.g. §
A.7.2
.)

Quantification:

Let
({\vec{a}}_{i})_{i=1,...,n}
be a basis in
E
,

({\vec{b}}_{i})_{i=1,...,m}
be a basis in
F
which dual basis is
({\pi_{bi}})
,

L\in{\cal L}(E;F)
. Then

\beta_{L}({\pi_{bi}},{\vec{a}}_{i})={\pi_{bi}}.L.{\vec{a}}_{i}.

(A.87)

Thus, if

L.{\vec{a}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}L_{ij}{\vec{b}}_{i}\quad\hbox{then}\quad\beta_{\!L}={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}L_{ij}{\vec{b}}_{i}\otimes\pi_{aj}

(A.88)

Indeed,

(\sum_{ij}L_{ij}{\vec{b}}_{i}\otimes\pi_{aj})({\pi_{bk}},{\vec{a}}_{\ell})=\sum_{ij}L_{ij}({\vec{b}}_{i}\otimes\pi_{aj})({\pi_{bk}},{\vec{a}}_{\ell})=\sum_{ij}L_{ij}({\vec{b}}_{i}.{\pi_{bk}})(\pi_{aj}.{\vec{a}}_{\ell})=\sum_{ij}L_{ij}({\vec{b}}_{i}.{\pi_{bk}})(\pi_{aj}.{\vec{a}}_{\ell})=\sum_{ij}L_{ij}\delta_{ki}\delta_{j\ell}=L_{k\ell}={\pi_{bk}}.L.{\vec{a}}_{\ell}
,
so (
A.87
) gives (
A.88
).

Duality notations:

L.{\vec{a}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}{L^{i}{}_{j}}{\vec{b}}_{i}
and
\beta_{\!L}={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}{L^{i}{}_{j}}{\vec{b}}_{i}\otimes a^{j}
.

Contraction rule.
If you write
L={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}L_{ij}{\vec{b}}_{i}\otimes\pi_{aj}
(
\simeq\beta_{L}
),
then the vector
L.{\vec{u}}\in F
is computed thanks to the “contraction rule”:

L.{\vec{u}}=({\sum_{i=1}^{m}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}L_{ij}{\vec{b}}_{i}\otimes\underbrace{\pi_{aj}).{\vec{u}}}_{\makebox[2.84544pt]{\footnotesize\rm contraction}}:={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}L_{ij}{\vec{b}}_{i}(\pi_{aj}.{\vec{u}})={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}L_{ij}u_{j}{\vec{b}}_{i}.

(A.89)

(With duality notations:

\displaystyle L.{\vec{u}}=({\sum_{i=1}^{m}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}L^{i}{}_{j}{\vec{b}}_{i}\otimes\underbrace{a^{j}).{\vec{u}}}_{\makebox[2.84544pt]{\footnotesize\rm contraction}}={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}L^{i}{}_{j}{\vec{b}}_{i}(a^{j}.{\vec{u}})={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}L^{i}{}_{j}u^{j}{\vec{b}}_{i}
.)

Remark A.69

Warning: The bilinear form
\beta_{\!L}
should not be confused with the linear map
L
: The domain of definition of
\beta_{\!L}
is
F^{*}\times E
, and
\beta_{\!L}
acts on the two objects
\ell
(linear form) and
{\vec{u}}
(vector) to get a
scalar
result; While the domain of definition of
L
is
E
, and
L
acts one object
{\vec{u}}
to get a
vector
result. However, you can use the tensorial notation for
L
… only to calculate
L.{\vec{u}}
with (
A.89
).

A.13.2
Warning: Confusion between transposed and adjoint

The transposed
L^{T}\in{\cal L}(F;E)
of a linear map
L\in{\cal L}(E;F)
needs inner dot products to be defined, cf (
A.68
): It is
not
intrinsic to
L
,
not
objective ; While the transposed
b^{T}\in{\cal L}(B,A;\mathbb{R})
of a bilinear form
b\in{\cal L}(A,B;\mathbb{R})
is intrinsic to
L
(it does not need inner dot products to be defined).

So if you represent a linear map
L\in{\cal L}(E;F)
by its tensorial representation
\beta_{\!L}\in{\cal L}(F^{*},E;\mathbb{R})
, cf. (
A.88
),
then

1- you know the transposed
(\beta_{\!L})^{T}
(given by
(\beta_{\!L})^{T}({\vec{w}},{\vec{u}})=\beta_{\!L}({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})
),

2- but you
cannot
deduce the transposed
L^{T}\in{\cal L}(F;E)
from
(\beta_{\!L})^{T}
(i.e., to start with
(\beta_{\!L})^{T}
is misleading): You need to choose inner dot products, and then use the formula
(L^{T}.{\vec{y}},{\vec{x}})_{g}=({\vec{y}},L.{\vec{x}})_{h}
where
L^{T}:=L^{T}_{gh}
to get
[L^{T}]=[g]^{-1}.[L]^{T}.[h]
(and
[L^{T}]\neq[L]^{T}
in general).

3- In particular: If
L\in{\cal L}(E;E)
is symmetric (relative to the chosen inner dot products), then
\beta_{\!L}\in{\cal L}(E^{*},E;\mathbb{R})
is never symmetric because
E^{*}\neq E\,
!
(Recall: there is no natural canonical isomorphism between
E
and
E^{*}
.)

A.14
Change of basis formulas for bilinear forms and linear maps

A.14.1
Notations for transitions matrices for bilinear forms and linear maps

Let
A
and
B
be finite dimension vector spaces,
\dim A=n
,
\dim B=m
.
(E.g. application to the change of basis formula for the deformation gradient
A{=}{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}\rightarrow B{=}{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
.)

Let
({{\vec{a}}_{old,i}})
and
({{\vec{a}}_{new,i}})
be two bases in
A
, and
({{\vec{b}}_{old,i}})
and
({{\vec{b}}_{new,i}})
be two bases in
B
.
Let
{\cal P}_{\!\!A}
and
{\cal P}_{\!\!B}
be the change of basis endomorphisms from old to new bases,
and
{P_{\!\!A}}:=[{\cal P}_{\!\!A}]_{|{{\vec{a}}_{old}}}=[{P_{\!\!A}}_{ij}]
and
{P_{\!\!B}}:=[{\cal P}_{\!\!B}]_{|{{\vec{b}}_{old}}}=[{P_{\!\!B}}_{ij}]
be the associated transition matrices, and
{Q_{\!\!A}}={P_{\!\!A}}^{-1}
and
{Q_{\!\!B}}={P_{\!\!B}}^{-1}
:

\displaystyle{{}{{\vec{a}}_{new,j}}={\cal P}_{\!\!A}.{{\vec{a}}_{old,i}}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}{P_{\!\!A}}_{ij}{{\vec{a}}_{old,i}},\quad\pi_{a{new},j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{Q_{\!\!A}}_{ij}\pi_{a{old},i},}

\displaystyle{{}{{\vec{b}}_{new,j}}={\cal P}_{\!\!B}.{{\vec{b}}_{old,i}}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}}{P_{\!\!B}}_{ij}{{\vec{b}}_{old,i}},\quad\pi_{b{new},j}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}{Q_{\!\!B}}_{ij}\pi_{b{old},i}.}

(A.90)

Duality notations:
{{\vec{a}}_{new,j}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{P_{\!\!A}}^{i}{}_{j}{{\vec{a}}_{old,i}}
and
{a^{i}_{new}}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}{Q_{\!\!A}}^{i}{}_{j}{a^{j}_{old}}

and
{{\vec{b}}_{new,j}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{P_{\!\!B}}^{i}{}_{j}{{\vec{b}}_{old,i}}
and
{b^{i}_{new}}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}{Q_{\!\!B}}^{i}{}_{j}{b^{j}_{old}}
.

A.14.2
Change of coordinate system for bilinear forms
\in{\cal L}(A,B;\mathbb{R})

Let
g\in{\cal L}(A,B;\mathbb{R})
, and, for all
(i,j)\in[1,n]_{\mathbb{N}}\times[1,m]_{\mathbb{N}}
,

g({{\vec{a}}_{old,i}},{{\vec{b}}_{old,j}})=M_{ij},\quad g({{\vec{a}}_{new,i}},{{\vec{b}}_{new,j}})=N_{ij},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&[g]_{|{old}s}=M=[M_{ij}]_{i=1,...,n\atop j=1,...,m},\cr&[g]_{|{new}s}=N=[N_{ij}]_{i=1,...,n\atop j=1,...,m}.\crcr}}\,\right.

(A.91)

Proposition A.70

Change of basis formula:

[g]_{|{new}s}={P_{\!\!A}}^{T}.[g]_{|{old}s}.{P_{\!\!B}},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad N={P_{\!\!A}}^{T}.M.{P_{\!\!B}}.

(A.92)

In particular, if
A=B
and
({{\vec{a}}_{old,i}})=({{\vec{b}}_{old,i}})
and
({{\vec{a}}_{new,i}})=({{\vec{b}}_{new,i}})
,
then
{P_{\!\!A}}={P_{\!\!B}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}P
, and

\framebox{$\displaystyle[g]_{|{new}}=P^{T}.[g]_{{old}}.P$},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad N=P^{T}.M.P.

(A.93)

Proof.
N_{ij}=g({{\vec{a}}_{new,i}},{{\vec{b}}_{new,j}})=\sum_{k\ell}{P_{\!\!A}}^{k}{}_{i}{P_{\!\!B}}^{\ell}{}_{j}g({{\vec{a}}_{old,k}},{{\vec{b}}_{old,\ell}})=\sum_{k\ell}{P_{\!\!A}}^{k}{}_{i}M_{k\ell}{P_{\!\!B}}^{\ell}{}_{j}=\sum_{k\ell}({P_{\!\!A}}^{T})^{i}{}_{k}M_{k\ell}{P_{\!\!B}}^{\ell}{}_{j}
.

Exercice A.71

Prove (objective result):

g({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})=[{\vec{u}}]_{|{{\vec{a}}_{new}}}^{T}.[g]_{|{new}s}.[{\vec{w}}]_{|{{\vec{b}}_{new}}}=[{\vec{u}}]_{|{{\vec{a}}_{old}}}^{T}.[g]_{|{old}s}.[{\vec{w}}]_{|{{\vec{b}}_{old}}}.

(A.94)

Answer
.
[{\vec{u}}]_{|{{\vec{a}}_{new}}}^{T}.[g]_{|{new}s}.[{\vec{w}}]_{|{{\vec{b}}_{new}}}=({P_{\!\!A}}^{-1}.[{\vec{u}}]_{|{{\vec{a}}_{old}}})^{T}.({P_{\!\!A}}^{T}.[g]_{|{old}s}.{P_{\!\!B}}).({P_{\!\!B}}^{-1}.[{\vec{w}}]_{|{{\vec{b}}_{old}}})
.

A.14.3
Change of coordinate system for bilinear forms
\in{\cal L}(A^{*},B^{*};\mathbb{R})

Let
z\in{\cal L}(A^{*},B^{*};\mathbb{R})
, and, for all
(i,j)\in[1,n]_{\mathbb{N}}\times[1,m]_{\mathbb{N}}
,

z({a^{i}_{old}},{b^{j}_{old}})=M^{ij},\quad z({a^{i}_{new}},{b^{j}_{new}})=N^{ij},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&[z]_{|{old}s}=M=[M^{ij}]_{i=1,...,n\atop j=1,...,m},\cr&[z]_{|{new}s}=N=[N^{ij}]_{i=1,...,n\atop j=1,...,m}.\crcr}}\,\right.

(A.95)

Proposition A.72

Change of basis formula:

[z]_{|{new}s}={P_{\!\!A}}^{-T}.[z]_{|{old}s}.{P_{\!\!B}}^{-1},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad N={P_{\!\!A}}^{-T}.M.{P_{\!\!B}}^{-1}.

(A.96)

In particular, if
A=B
and
({{\vec{a}}_{old,i}})=({{\vec{b}}_{old,i}})
and
({{\vec{a}}_{new,i}})=({{\vec{b}}_{new,i}})
,
then
{P_{\!\!A}}={P_{\!\!B}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}P
, and

\framebox{$\displaystyle[z]_{|{new}}=P^{-T}.[z]_{{old}}.P^{-1}$},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad N=P^{-T}.M.P^{-1}.

(A.97)

Proof.
N_{ij}=z({a^{i}_{new}},{b^{j}_{new}})=\sum_{k\ell}{Q_{\!\!A}}^{k}{}_{i}{Q_{\!\!B}}^{\ell}_{j}z({a^{k}_{old}},{b^{\ell}_{old}})=\sum_{k\ell}{Q_{\!\!A}}^{k}{}_{i}M^{k\ell}{Q_{\!\!B}}^{\ell}{}_{j}=\sum_{k\ell}({Q_{\!\!A}}^{T})^{i}{}_{k}M^{k\ell}{Q_{\!\!B}}^{\ell}{}_{j}
.

A.14.4
Change of coordinate system for bilinear forms
\in{\cal L}(B^{*},A;\mathbb{R})

(Toward linear maps
L\in{\cal L}(A;B)\simeq{\cal L}(B^{*},A;\mathbb{R})
thanks to the natural canonical isomorphism.)

Let
T\in{\cal L}(B^{*},A;\mathbb{R})
, and, for all
(i,j)\in[1,n]_{\mathbb{N}}\times[1,m]_{\mathbb{N}}
,

T({b^{i}_{old}},{{\vec{a}}_{old,j}})=M^{i}{}_{j},\quad T({b^{i}_{new}},{{\vec{a}}_{new,j}})=N^{i}{}_{j},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&[T]_{|{old}s}=M=[M^{i}{}_{j}]_{i=1,...,n\atop j=1,...,m},\cr&[T]_{|{new}s}=N=[N^{i}{}_{j}]_{i=1,...,n\atop j=1,...,m}.\crcr}}\,\right.

(A.98)

Proposition A.73

Change of basis formula:

[T]_{|{new}s}={P_{\!\!B}}^{-1}.[T]_{|{old}s}.{P_{\!\!A}},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad N={Q_{\!\!A}}.M.{P_{\!\!B}}.

(A.99)

In particular, if
A=B
and
({{\vec{a}}_{old,i}})=({{\vec{b}}_{old,i}})
and
({{\vec{a}}_{new,i}})=({{\vec{b}}_{new,i}})
,
then
{P_{\!\!A}}={P_{\!\!B}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}P
, and

\framebox{$\displaystyle[T]_{|{new}}=P^{-1}.[T]_{{old}}.P$},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad N=P^{-1}.M.P,\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad N^{i}{}_{j}={\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{n}}Q^{i}{}_{k}M^{k}{}_{\ell}P^{\ell}{}_{j}.

(A.100)

Proof.
N^{i}{}_{j}=T({b^{i}_{new}},{{\vec{a}}_{new,j}})=\sum_{k\ell}{Q_{\!\!B}}^{i}{}_{k}{P_{\!\!A}}^{\ell}{}_{j}T({b^{i}_{old}},{{\vec{a}}_{old,j}})=\sum_{k\ell}{Q_{\!\!B}}^{i}{}_{k}M^{i}{}_{j}{P_{\!\!A}}^{\ell}{}_{j}

A.14.5
Change of coordinate system for tri-linear forms
\in{\cal L}(A^{*},A,A;\mathbb{R})

(Toward
d^{2}{\vec{u}}
: For a vector field
{\vec{u}}\in\Gamma(U)\simeq{T^{1}_{0}(U)}
,
{\vec{u}}(p)\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
,
its differential satisfies
d{\vec{u}}(p)\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})\simeq{\cal L}({{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{*}},{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}};\mathbb{R})
,
and
d^{2}{\vec{u}}(p)\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}};{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}))\simeq{\cal L}({{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{*}},{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}},{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}};\mathbb{R})
, see §
S.1.3
.)

Consider a tri-linear form
T\in{\cal L}(A^{*},A,A;\mathbb{R})
, and

M^{i}_{jk}=T({a^{i}_{old}},{{\vec{a}}_{old,j}},{{\vec{a}}_{old,k}}),\quad N^{i}_{jk}=T({a^{i}_{new}},{{\vec{a}}_{new,j}},{{\vec{a}}_{new,k}}),\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad[T]_{|{{\vec{a}}_{old}}}=[M^{i}_{jk}],\quad[T]_{|{{\vec{a}}_{new}}}=[N^{i}_{jk}].

(A.101)

Then

N^{i}_{jk}=\sum_{\lambda,\mu,\nu=1}^{n}Q^{i}_{\lambda}P^{\mu}_{j}P^{\nu}_{k}M^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}.

(A.102)

Indeed

\sum_{\lambda\mu\nu}M^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}{\vec{a}}_{old,\lambda}\otimes a_{old}^{\mu}\otimes a_{old}^{\nu}=\sum_{\lambda\mu\nu ijk}M^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}Q^{i}_{\lambda}P^{\mu}_{j}P^{\nu}_{k}{{\vec{a}}_{new,i}}\otimes{a^{j}_{new}}\otimes{a^{k}_{new}}
.

A.14.6
Change of coordinate system for linear maps
\in{\cal L}(A;B)

Notation of §
A.14.1
.
Let
L\in{\cal L}(A;B)
be a linear map, and let, for all
j=1,...,n
,

\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&L.{{\vec{a}}_{old,j}}={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}M_{ij}{{\vec{b}}_{old,i}}={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}M^{i}{}_{j}{{\vec{b}}_{old,i}}\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad[L]_{|olds}=M=[M_{ij}]=[M^{i}{}_{j}]_{i=1,...,m\atop j=1,...,n},\cr&L.{{\vec{a}}_{new,j}}={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}N_{ij}{{\vec{b}}_{new,i}}={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}N^{i}{}_{j}{{\vec{b}}_{new,i}}\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad[L]_{|news}=N=[N_{ij}]=[N^{i}{}_{j}]_{i=1,...,m\atop j=1,...,n},\cr}}\,\right.

(A.103)

with classical and duality notations.

Proposition A.74

Change of bases formula:

[L]_{|{new}s}={P_{\!\!B}}^{-1}.[L]_{|{old}s}.{P_{\!\!A}},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad N={P_{\!\!B}}^{-1}.M.{P_{\!\!A}}.

(A.104)

In particular, if
A=B
, if
({{\vec{a}}_{old,i}})=({{\vec{b}}_{old,i}})
,
({{\vec{a}}_{new,i}})=({{\vec{b}}_{new,i}})
, then

{P_{\!\!A}}={P_{\!\!B}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}P
and

\framebox{$\displaystyle[L]_{|{new}}=P^{-1}.[L]_{|{old}}.P$},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad N=P^{-1}.M.P,\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad N_{ij}=\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{n}Q_{ik}M_{k\ell}P_{\ell j},

(A.105)

with
Q=P^{-1}
, and with duality notations
N^{i}{}_{j}=\sum_{k\ell}Q^{i}{}_{k}M^{k}{}_{\ell}P^{\ell}{}_{j}
.

Proof.
L.{{\vec{a}}_{new,j}}=\sum_{i}N^{i}{}_{j}{{\vec{b}}_{new,i}}=\sum_{ik}N^{i}{}_{j}{P_{\!\!B}}^{k}{}_{i}{{\vec{b}}_{old,k}}=\sum_{k}({P_{\!\!B}}.N)^{k}{}_{j}{{\vec{b}}_{old,k}}
and

L.{{\vec{a}}_{new,j}}=L.(\sum_{i}{P_{\!\!A}}^{i}{}_{j}{{\vec{a}}_{old,i}})=\sum_{i}{P_{\!\!A}}^{i}{}_{j}\sum_{k}M^{k}{}_{i}{{\vec{b}}_{old,k}}=\sum_{k}(M.{P_{\!\!A}})^{k}{}_{j}{{\vec{b}}_{old,k}}
, for all
j
, thus
{P_{\!\!B}}.N=M.{P_{\!\!A}}
.

Exercice A.75

Prove:

\ell.L.{\vec{u}}=[\ell]_{|{{\vec{b}}_{new}}}.[L]_{|{new}s}.[{\vec{u}}]_{|{{\vec{a}}_{new}}}=[\ell]_{|{{\vec{b}}_{old}}}.[L]_{|{old}s}.[{\vec{u}}]_{|{{\vec{a}}_{old}}}\quad(\hbox{objective result}).

(A.106)

Answer
.
[\ell]_{|{{\vec{b}}_{new}}}.[L]_{|{new}s}.[{\vec{u}}]_{|{{\vec{a}}_{new}}}=([\ell]_{|{{\vec{b}}_{old}}}.{P_{\!\!B}}).({P_{\!\!B}}^{-1}.[L]_{|{old}s}.{P_{\!\!A}}).({P_{\!\!A}}^{-1}.[{\vec{u}}]_{|{{\vec{a}}_{old}}})
.

Remark A.76

Bilinear forms in
{\cal L}(A,A;\mathbb{R})
and endomorphisms in
{\cal L}(A;A)

behave differently: The formulas (
A.93
) and (
A.105
) should not be confused
since
P^{-1}\neq P^{T}
in general.
E.g., if an English observer uses a Euclidean (old) basis
({\vec{a}}_{i})=({{\vec{a}}_{old,i}})
in foot,
if a French observer uses a Euclidean (new) basis
({\vec{b}}_{i})=({{\vec{a}}_{new,i}})
in metre,
and if (simple case)
{\vec{b}}_{i}=\lambda{\vec{a}}_{i}
for all
i
(change of unit),
then

[L]_{|{new}}=[L]_{|{old}},\quad\hbox{while}\quad[g]_{|{new}}=\underbrace{\lambda^{2}}_{>10}\;[g]_{|{old}}.

(A.107)

Quite different results! I.e.
P^{-1}.[L]_{|{old}}.P\neq P^{T}.[L]_{|{old}}.P
for a general change of basis. See the Mars Climate Orbiter crash, remark
A.14
, where someone forgot that 1 foot
\neq
1 metre.

Appendix B
Euclidean Frameworks

Time and space are decoupled (classical mechanics).

{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
is the geometric affine space,
n=1,2,3
,
and
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
is the associated vector space made of “bi-point vectors”.

B.1
Euclidean basis

Manufacturing of a Euclidean basis
.

An observer chooses a unit of measure (foot, metre, a unit of length used by Euclid, the diameter a of pipe…)
and makes a “unit rod” of length
1
in this unit.

Postulate: The length of the rod does not depend on its direction in space.

\bullet
Space dimension
n=1
:
This rod models a vector
{\vec{e}}_{1}
which makes a basis
({\vec{e}}_{1})
called the Euclidean basis relative
to the chosen unit of measure.

\bullet
Space dimension
n\geq 2
:

- The observers makes three rods of length 3, 4 and 5,
and makes a triangle
(A,B,C)
with
A
,
B
and
C
are the vertices and
A
not on the side on length
5
.

- Pythagoras:
3^{2}+4^{2}=5^{2}
gives: The triangle
(A,B,C)
is said to have a right angle at
A
.

- Two vectors
{\vec{u}}
and
{\vec{w}}
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
are orthogonal iff the triangle
(A,B,C)

can be positioned such that
\vec{AB}
and
\vec{AC}
are parallel to
{\vec{u}}
and
{\vec{w}}
.

- A basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})_{i=1,...,n}
is Euclidean relative to the chosen unit of measurement
iff the
{\vec{e}}_{i}
are two to two orthogonal and their length is
1
(relative to the chosen unit).

Example B.1

An English observer defines a Euclidean basis
({\vec{a}}_{i})
using the foot.
A French observer defines a Euclidean basis
({\vec{b}}_{i})
using the metre. We have

1\,{\rm foot}=\mu\,{\rm metre},\quad\mu=0.3048,\quad\hbox{and}\quad 1\,{\rm metre}=\lambda\,{\rm foot},\quad\lambda={1\over\mu}\simeq 3.28.

(B.1)

(
\mu=0,3048
is the official length in metre for the English foot.)
E.g., the bases are “aligned” iff, for all
i
,

{\vec{b}}_{i}=\lambda{\vec{a}}_{i}\quad(\hbox{change of measurement unit}),

(B.2)

thus the transition matrix from
({\vec{a}}_{i})
to
({\vec{b}}_{i})
is
P=\lambda I
, thus
P^{T}=P
,
P^{-1}={1\over\lambda}I
and
P^{T}.P=\lambda^{2}I
.

Remark B.2

The bases used in practice are not all Euclidean.
See example
A.13
, especially if you fly.

B.2
Euclidean dot product

Definition B.3

An observer who has built) his Euclidean basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
, cf. §
B.1
.
The associated Euclidean dot product is the bilinear form
g(\cdot,\cdot)=(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}},{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}};\mathbb{R})
defined by

(g_{ij}=)\quad g({\vec{e}}_{i},{\vec{e}}_{j})=\delta_{ij},\quad\forall i,j,\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[\delta_{ij}]=I.

(B.3)

In other words,

(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}:={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\pi_{ei}\otimes\pi_{ei}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}e^{i}\otimes e^{i},

(B.4)

with classical and duality notations,
(\pi_{ei})=(e^{i})
being the dual basis of
({\vec{e}}_{i})
.
And if you want to use the Einstein convention you have to write
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}:={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}\delta_{ij}e^{i}\otimes e^{j}
: You cannot avoid writing
\delta_{ij}=g_{ij}
.

Thus, for all
{\vec{x}},{\vec{y}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
, with
{\vec{x}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
and
{\vec{y}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}y_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
(classical notations),

({\vec{x}},{\vec{y}})_{g}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x_{i}y_{i}=[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}.[{\vec{y}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}.

(B.5)

With duality notations,
{\vec{x}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,
{\vec{y}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}y^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
and
({\vec{x}},{\vec{y}})_{g}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x^{i}y^{i}
;
And if you want to use the Einstein convention then write
({\vec{x}},{\vec{y}})_{g}:={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}\delta_{ij}x^{i}y^{j}
: You cannot avoid writing
\delta_{ij}
.

Definition B.4

The associated norm is
||.||_{g}:=\sqrt{(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}}
, and the length of a vector
{\vec{x}}
relative to the chosen Euclidean unit of measurement is
||{\vec{x}}||_{g}:=\sqrt{({\vec{x}},{\vec{x}})_{g}}
.

Thus with the Euclidean basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
(used to build
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
), if
{\vec{x}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
, then
||{\vec{x}}||_{g}=\sqrt{{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x_{i}^{2}}
is the length of
{\vec{x}}
relative to the chosen Euclidean unit of measure (Pythagoras).
(With duality notations
||{\vec{x}}||_{g}=\sqrt{{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}(x^{i})^{2}}
, and if you want to use the Einstein convention:
||{\vec{x}}||_{g}=\sqrt{{\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}\delta_{ij}x^{i}x^{j}}
.)

Definition B.5

The angle
\theta({\vec{x}},{\vec{y}})
between two vectors
{\vec{x}},{\vec{y}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}-\{\vec{0}\}
is defined by

\cos(\theta({\vec{x}},{\vec{y}}))=({{\vec{x}}\over||{\vec{x}}||_{g}},{{\vec{y}}\over||{\vec{y}}||_{g}})_{g}.

(B.6)

(With a calculator, this formula gives
\theta({\vec{x}},{\vec{y}})=\arccos(({{\vec{x}}\over||{\vec{x}}||_{g}},{{\vec{y}}\over||{\vec{y}}||_{g}})_{g})

a value in
[0,\pi]
.)

B.3
Change of Euclidean basis

Let
({\vec{a}}_{i})
(e.g. English observer basis built with the foot) and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
(e.g. French observer basis built with the metre) be Euclidean bases in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
, and let
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
be the associated Euclidean dot products.

B.3.1
Two Euclidean dot products are proportional

Proposition B.6

If
\lambda=||{\vec{b}}_{1}||_{g}
, then
||{\vec{b}}_{i}||_{g}=\lambda
for all
i=1,...,n
(change of unit) and

(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}=\lambda^{2}(\cdot,\cdot)_{h},\quad\hbox{and}\quad||.||_{g}=\lambda||.||_{h}.

(B.7)

Proof.
By definition of a Euclidean basis, the length of the rod that enabled to define
({\vec{b}}_{i})
is independent of
i
, cf. §
B.1
, thus
||{\vec{b}}_{i}||_{g}=||{\vec{b}}_{1}||_{g}
for all
i
, and here
||{\vec{b}}_{i}||_{g}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\lambda
.
Thus
||{\vec{b}}_{i}||_{g}^{2}=\lambda^{2}=\lambda^{2}||{\vec{b}}_{i}||_{h}^{2}
for all
i
, since
||{\vec{b}}_{i}||^{2}_{h}=1
.
And if
i\neq j
then
({\vec{b}}_{i},{\vec{b}}_{j})_{g}=0=({\vec{b}}_{i},{\vec{b}}_{j})_{h}
since
{\vec{b}}_{i}
and
{\vec{b}}_{j}
form a right angle (Pythagoras), cf. (
B.4
).
Hence
({\vec{b}}_{i},{\vec{b}}_{j})_{g}=\lambda^{2}({\vec{b}}_{i},{\vec{b}}_{j})_{h}
for all
i,j
, thus (
B.7
).

Example B.7

Continuation of example
B.1
:
(\cdot,\cdot)_{a}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}a^{i}\otimes a^{i}
is the English Euclidean dot product (foot),
and

(\cdot,\cdot)_{b}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}b^{i}\otimes b^{i}
is the French Euclidean dot product (metre).
(
B.7
) and (
B.1
) give:

(\cdot,\cdot)_{a}=\lambda^{2}(\cdot,\cdot)_{b}\quad\hbox{and}\quad||.||_{a}=\lambda||.||_{b},\quad\hbox{with}\quad\lambda\simeq 3.28\quad\hbox{and}\quad\lambda^{2}\simeq 10.76.

(B.8)

In particular, if
{\vec{w}}
is s.t.
||{\vec{w}}||_{b}=1
(its length is
1
metre),
then
||{\vec{w}}||_{a}=\lambda
(its length is
\lambda\simeq 3.28
foot).

B.3.2
Counterexample : non existence of a Euclidean dot product

1- Thermodynamic: Let
T
be the temperature and
P
the pressure,
and consider the Cartesian vector space
\{(T,P)\}=\{\hbox{(temperature,pressure)}\}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
.
There is no associated Euclidean dot product: An associated norm would give

||(T,P)||=\sqrt{T^{2}+P^{2}}\in\mathbb{R}
which is meaningless (incompatible dimensions). See §
A.3.5
.

2- Polar coordinate system
{\vec{q}}=(r,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
: There is no Euclidean norm
\sqrt{r^{2}+\theta^{2}}
for
{\vec{q}}
that is physically meaningful (incompatible dimensions), see example
6.11
.

B.4
Euclidean transposed of the deformation gradient

Let
n\in\{1,2,3\}
and consider a linear map
L\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})
(e.g.,
L={F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
).

Let
(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}
be a Euclidean dot product in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
(used in the past by someone), and let

(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
be Euclidean dot products in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
(the actual space where the results are obtained
by two observers, e.g.,
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
built with a foot and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
built with a metre).
Let
L^{T}_{Gg}
and
L^{T}_{Gh}
be the transposed of
L
relative to the dot products,
that is,
L^{T}_{Gg}
and
L^{T}_{Gh}
in
{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}})
are characterized by,
for all
({\vec{X}},{\vec{y}})\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}\times{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
, cf. (
A.68
),

(L^{T}_{Gg}.{\vec{y}},{\vec{X}})_{G}=(L.{\vec{X}},{\vec{y}})_{g}\quad\hbox{and}\quad(L^{T}_{Gh}.{\vec{y}},{\vec{X}})_{G}=(L.{\vec{X}},{\vec{y}})_{h}.

(B.9)

Corollary B.8

\hbox{if}\quad(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}=\lambda^{2}(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}\quad\hbox{then}\quad L_{Gg}^{T}=\lambda^{2}L_{Gh}^{T}.

(B.10)

NB: Do not forget
\lambda^{2}
, cf. remark
A.14
(Mars Climate Orbiter crash).

Proof.
\displaystyle(L_{Gg}^{T}.{\vec{y}},{\vec{X}})_{G}\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqgagbpropG})}(L.{\vec{X}},{\vec{y}})_{g}\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqteG})_{1}}\lambda^{2}(L.{\vec{X}},{\vec{y}})_{h}\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqgagbpropG})}\lambda^{2}(L^{T}_{Gh}.{\vec{y}},{\vec{X}})_{G}
for all
{\vec{X}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
and all
{\vec{y}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
, thus
L_{Gg}^{T}.{\vec{y}}=\lambda^{2}L_{Gh}^{T}.{\vec{y}}
for all
{\vec{y}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
,
thus (
B.10
)
2
.

B.5
The Euclidean transposed for endomorphisms

Let
n\in\{1,2,3\}
and consider an endomorphism
L\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})

(e.g.
L=d{\vec{v}}_{t}(p)\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})
the differential of the Eulerian velocity).
Let
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
be dot products in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
.
Let
L^{T}_{g}
and
L^{T}_{h}
be the transposed of
L
relative to
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
,
that is,
L^{T}_{g}
and
L^{T}_{h}
in
{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})
are the endomorphisms defined by, for all
{\vec{x}},{\vec{y}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
, cf. (
A.54
),

(L^{T}_{g}.{\vec{y}},{\vec{x}})_{g}=(L.{\vec{x}},{\vec{y}})_{g},\quad\hbox{and}\quad(L^{T}_{h}.{\vec{y}},{\vec{x}})_{h}=(L.{\vec{x}},{\vec{y}})_{h}.

(B.11)

Corollary B.9

\hbox{if}\quad(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}=\lambda^{2}(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}\quad\hbox{then}\quad L_{g}^{T}=L_{h}^{T}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}L^{T}\quad\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})

(B.12)

(an endomorphism type relation):
Thus we can speak of “the Euclidean transposed of an endomorphism”.

Proof.
\displaystyle(L_{g}^{T}.{\vec{y}},{\vec{x}})_{g}\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqgagbprop0})}(L.{\vec{x}},{\vec{y}})_{g}\mathop{=}^{hyp}\lambda^{2}(L.{\vec{x}},{\vec{y}})_{h}\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqgagbprop0})}\lambda^{2}(L^{T}_{h}.{\vec{y}},{\vec{x}})_{h}\mathop{=}^{hyp}(L^{T}_{h}.{\vec{y}},{\vec{x}})_{g}
for all
{\vec{x}},{\vec{y}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
, thus
L_{g}^{T}.{\vec{y}}=L_{h}^{T}.{\vec{y}}
for all
{\vec{y}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
.

B.6
Unit normal vector, unit normal form

The results in this § are not objective:
We need a Euclidean dot product (need a unit of length: Foot? Meter?) to get a unit (Euclidean) normal vector.

B.6.1
Framework

(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
is a Euclidean dot product (needed to define Euclidean orthonormality) and, for all
{\vec{u}},{\vec{w}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
,

({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})_{g}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{u}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,{\vec{w}}

(B.13)

(or
{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{u}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{\vec{w}}
when one chosen Euclidean dot product is imposed to all).

\Omega
is a regular open bounded set in
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
,
n=2
or
3
, and
\Gamma:=\partial\Omega
is its regular surface (dimension
n{-}1
). If
p\in\Gamma
then

T_{p}\Gamma
is the tangent plane at
p
to
\Gamma
,
and a basis
({\vec{\beta}}_{1}(p),...,{\vec{\beta}}_{n-1}(p))
in
T_{p}\Gamma
is known (usually obtained thanks to a coordinate system describing
\Gamma
).
And, to lighten the writings,
({\vec{\beta}}_{1}(p),...,{\vec{\beta}}_{n-1}(p))
is written
({\vec{\beta}}_{1},...,{\vec{\beta}}_{n-1})
.

B.6.2
Unit normal vector

Call
{\vec{n}}_{g}(p)
the unit outward normal vector at
p\in\Gamma
at
T_{p}\Gamma
relative to
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
; So
{\vec{n}}_{g}(p)\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,{\vec{\beta}}_{i}(p)=0
for all
i=1,...,n{-}1
, and
||{\vec{n}}_{g}(p)||_{g}=1
, i.e.
{\vec{n}}_{g}
is defined on
\Gamma
by (up to its sign)

\forall i=1,...,n{-}1,\;\;{\vec{\beta}}_{i}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,{\vec{n}}_{g}=0,\quad\hbox{and}\quad{\vec{n}}_{g}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,{\vec{n}}_{g}=1\quad(=||{\vec{n}}_{g}||_{g}^{2}),

(B.14)

i.e., at any
p\in\Gamma
,
{\vec{n}}_{g}(p)
is orthogonal to the hyperplane
{\rm Vect}\{{\vec{\beta}}_{1}(p),...,{\vec{\beta}}_{n-1}(p)\}
and
{\vec{n}}_{g}(p)
is unitary.
So
({\vec{\beta}}_{1}(p),...,{\vec{\beta}}_{n-1}(p),{\vec{n}}_{g}(p))
is a basis at
p
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
, written in short
({\vec{\beta}}_{1},...,{\vec{\beta}}_{n-1},{\vec{n}}_{g})
.
Drawing.

Thus, for all
{\vec{w}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
, if
{\vec{w}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}w_{i}{\vec{\beta}}_{i}+w_{n}{\vec{n}}_{g}
(classical notations) then

w_{n}={\vec{w}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,{\vec{n}}_{g}=\hbox{ the normal component of\penalty\ ${\vec{w}}$ at\penalty\ $p$ at\penalty\ $\Gamma$}.

(B.15)

(
w_{n}
depends on
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
.)
(Duality notations:
{\vec{w}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}w^{i}{\vec{\beta}}_{i}+w^{n}{\vec{n}}_{g}
and
w^{n}={\vec{w}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,{\vec{n}}_{g}
.)

Exercice B.10

Let
({\vec{a}}_{i})
be a basis in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
,
{\vec{\beta}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}B_{ij}{\vec{a}}_{i}
for
j=1,...,n{-}1
, and
{\vec{n}}_{g}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}n_{i}{\vec{a}}_{i}
,
and
g_{ij}=g({\vec{a}}_{i},{\vec{a}}_{j})
for all
i,j
.
What equations satisfy the
n_{j}
? And particular case
({\vec{a}}_{i})
is
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-orthonormal?

Answer
. (
B.14
) gives
[{\vec{\beta}}_{i}]_{|{\vec{a}}}^{T}.[g]_{|{\vec{a}}}.[{\vec{n}}_{g}]_{|{\vec{a}}}=0
for
i=1,...,n{-}1
(so
n{-}1
equations), with
[{\vec{n}}_{g}]_{|{\vec{a}}}^{T}.[g]_{|{\vec{a}}}.[{\vec{n}}_{g}]_{|{\vec{a}}}=1
(so 1 equation),
and
{\vec{n}}_{g}
is obtained up to its sign.

If
({\vec{a}}_{i})
is
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-orthonormal, then
{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}B_{ij}n_{j}=0
for
j=1,...,n{-}1
, with
{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}n_{i}^{2}=1
.

Exercice B.11

Let
({\vec{a}}_{i})
be a Euclidean basis in foot,
({\vec{b}}_{i})
a Euclidean basis in metre,

(\cdot,\cdot)_{a}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{b}
the associated Euclidean dot products, so
(\cdot,\cdot)_{a}=\lambda^{2}(\cdot,\cdot)_{b}
with
\lambda\simeq 3.28
, cf. (
B.7
).
Let
{\vec{n}}_{a}(p)
and
{\vec{n}}_{b}(p)
be the corresponding unit outward normal vectors, cf. (
B.14
).
1- Prove (up to the sign):

{\vec{n}}_{b}=\lambda{\vec{n}}_{a},\quad\hbox{and}\quad({\vec{w}},{\vec{n}}_{a})_{a}=\lambda({\vec{w}},{\vec{n}}_{b})_{b}\quad\forall{\vec{w}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}

(B.16)

2- Then let
{\vec{n}}_{a}={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}n_{ai}{\vec{a}}_{i}
and
{\vec{n}}_{b}={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}n_{bi}{\vec{b}}_{i}
; Prove:

\hbox{If},\;\forall i=1,...,n,\;{\vec{b}}_{i}=\lambda{\vec{a}}_{i}\quad\hbox{then}\quad\forall i=1,...,n,\;n_{ai}=n_{bi}.

(B.17)

So the vectors
{\vec{n}}_{a}
and
{\vec{n}}_{b}
are different (
\lambda>1
), and their respective components are equal… relative to different bases! And of course
1=||{\vec{n}}_{a}||_{a}^{2}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}(n_{ai})^{2}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}(n_{bi})^{2}=||{\vec{n}}_{b}||_{b}^{2}=1
.

Answer
.
{\vec{n}}_{a}(p)\parallel{\vec{n}}_{b}(p)
, since the vectors are Euclidean and orthogonal to
T_{p}\Gamma
cf. (
B.14
).
And
||.||_{a}=\lambda||.||_{b}
cf. (
B.8
),
thus
||{\vec{n}}_{b}||_{b}=1=||{\vec{n}}_{a}||_{a}=\lambda||{\vec{n}}_{a}||_{b}=||\lambda{\vec{n}}_{a}||_{b}
,
so
{\vec{n}}_{b}=\pm\lambda{\vec{n}}_{a}
.
And they both are outward vectors, so
{\vec{n}}_{b}=+\lambda{\vec{n}}_{a}
.
Thus
({\vec{w}},{\vec{n}}_{a})_{a}=\lambda^{2}({\vec{w}},{\vec{n}}_{a})_{b}=\lambda^{2}({\vec{w}},{{\vec{n}}_{b}\over\lambda})_{b}=\lambda({\vec{w}},{\vec{n}}_{b})_{b}
.

And if
{\vec{b}}_{i}=\lambda{\vec{a}}_{i}
(
B.16
) gives

{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}n^{i}_{b}{\vec{b}}_{i}=\lambda{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}n^{i}_{a}{\vec{a}}_{i}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}n^{i}_{a}(\lambda{\vec{a}}_{i})={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}n^{i}_{a}{\vec{b}}_{i}
, then
n_{a}^{i}=n_{b}^{i}
.

B.6.3
Unit normal form
n^{\flat}
associated to
{\vec{n}}

(For mathematicians; May produce misunderstandings and lack of mechanical interpretations; Don’t forget:
n^{\flat}
is obtained after
{\vec{n}}
has been defined.)

At
p\in\Gamma
, once you have computed
{\vec{n}}_{g}(p)
, you can define the associated unit normal form
n^{\flat}_{g}(p)\in{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{*}}
: It is the linear form defined by

n^{\flat}_{g}(p).{\vec{w}}:={\vec{n}}_{g}(p)\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,{\vec{w}}
for all
{\vec{w}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
, i.e. on
\Gamma
, for all
{\vec{w}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
,

n^{\flat}_{g}.{\vec{w}}:={\vec{n}}_{g}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,{\vec{w}}

(B.18)

(
{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{n}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{\vec{w}}
if one chosen Euclidean dot product is imposed to all).
Thus
[n^{\flat}_{g}].[{\vec{w}}]=[{\vec{n}}_{g}]^{T}.[g].[{\vec{w}}]
.

Quantification: Let
({\vec{e}}_{i})
be a basis in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
; Then (
B.18
) gives

[n^{\flat}_{g}]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[{\vec{n}}_{g}]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}.[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}
simply written
[n^{\flat}_{g}].[{\vec{w}}]=[{\vec{n}}_{g}]^{T}.[g].[{\vec{w}}]
if the basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is imposed.

So, with duality notations to justify the
♭
notation, with

(e^{i})
the dual basis of
({\vec{e}}_{i})
, let

{\vec{n}}_{g}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}n_{g}^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}\quad\hbox{and}\quad n_{g}^{\flat}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}n_{gi}e^{i}.

(B.19)

i.e.
n_{g}^{i}
and
n_{gi}
are the components of
{\vec{n}}_{g}
and
n_{g}^{\flat}
relative to the basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
and
(e^{i})
.
Since (
B.18
) gives
n^{\flat}_{g}.{\vec{e}}_{i}:={\vec{n}}_{g}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,{\vec{e}}_{i}
for all
i
, we get, for all
i
,

n_{ig}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}g_{ij}n_{g}^{j}

(B.20)

Particular case
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Euclidean basis, then
n_{ig}=n_{g}^{i}
.

Classical notations:
{\vec{n}}_{g}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}({\vec{n}}_{g})_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
, dual basis
(\pi_{ei})
,
n_{g}^{\flat}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}(n_{g}^{\flat})_{i}\pi_{ei}
,
(n_{g}^{\flat})_{i}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}g_{ij}({\vec{n}}_{g})_{j}
.

NB: In physics don’t forget to write the
g_{ij}
in (
B.20
) even if
g_{ij}=\delta_{ij}
, since you need to see the chosen metric and basis (and verify the Einstein convention), although (
B.20
) is simply written
n_{i}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}g_{ij}n^{j}
…

B.7
Integration by parts (Green–Gauss–Ostrogradsky)

Let
\Omega
be a regular bounded open set in
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
and
\Gamma=\partial\Omega
its frontier,
let
\varphi\in C^{1}(\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{R})
, let
({\vec{e}}_{i})
be a Euclidean basis and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
ites associated Euclidean dot product, let
{\partial\varphi\over\partial x_{i}}(p):=d\varphi(p).{\vec{e}}_{i}
(usual notation),
let
{\vec{n}}_{g}(p)={\vec{n}}(p)={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}n_{i}(p){\vec{e}}_{i}
(classical notations) be the unit outward normal at
p\in\Gamma
.
Then, for
i=1,...,n
,

\int_{p\in\Omega}{\partial\varphi\over\partial x_{i}}(p)\,d\Omega=\int_{p\in\Gamma}\varphi(p)n_{i}(p)\,d\Gamma,\quad\hbox{in short}\quad\int_{\Omega}{\partial\varphi\over\partial x_{i}}\,d\Omega=\int_{\Gamma}\varphi n_{i}\,d\Gamma.

(B.21)

Thus, for any
v\in C^{1}(\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{R})
, with
\varphi v
instead of
\varphi
in (
B.21
), we get the integration by parts formula (Green formula):

\int_{\Omega}{\partial\varphi\over\partial x_{i}}v\,d\Omega=-\int_{\Omega}\varphi\,{\partial v\over\partial x_{i}}\,d\Omega+\int_{\Gamma}\varphi vn_{i}\,d\Gamma.

(B.22)

Thus, for any
{\vec{v}}\in C^{1}(\overline{\Omega};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})
(vector field), with
{\vec{v}}(p)={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}v_{i}(p){\vec{e}}_{i})
we get

\int_{\Omega}{\partial\varphi\over\partial x_{i}}v_{i}\,d\Omega=-\int_{\Omega}\varphi\,{\partial v_{i}\over\partial x_{i}}\,d\Omega+\int_{\Gamma}\varphi v_{i}n_{i}\,d\Gamma.

(B.23)

Thus, with the gradient vector
{\vec{\rm grad}}\varphi(p)={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\partial\varphi\over\partial x_{i}}{\vec{e}}_{i}
and with
{\rm div}{\vec{v}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\partial v_{i}\over\partial x_{i}}
, we get the Gauss–Ostrogradsky formula:

\int_{\Omega}{\vec{\rm grad}}\varphi\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{\vec{v}}\,d\Omega=-\int_{\Omega}\varphi\,{\rm div}{\vec{v}}\,d\Omega+\int_{\Gamma}\varphi{\vec{v}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{\vec{n}}\,d\Gamma.

(B.24)

(And
\int_{\Gamma}\varphi{\vec{v}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{\vec{n}}\,d\Gamma
gives the flux through
\Gamma
.)

Exercice B.12

Use the differential
d\varphi
instead of the gradient
{\vec{\rm grad}}\varphi
(which is the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Riesz representation vector of
d\varphi
) to express (
B.23
). Is the use of
n^{\flat}
useful in that case?

Answer
.
\int_{\Omega}d\varphi.{\vec{v}}\,d\Omega=-\int_{\Omega}\varphi\,{\rm div}{\vec{v}}\,d\Omega+\int_{\Gamma}\varphi{\vec{v}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{\vec{n}}\,d\Gamma
.
Since
n^{\flat}
depends on
{\vec{n}}
(definition), there is no reason that justifies the use of
n^{\flat}
(unless you want to introduce useless notations here).

Appendix C
Rate of deformation tensor and spin tensor

Let
{\widetilde{\Phi}}:[t_{1},t_{2}]\times{\it O\!b\!j}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
be a regular motion, cf. (
1.5
),
and let
{\vec{v}}:\hbox{\large$\cal C$}\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
be the Eulerian velocity field, cf. (
2.4
),
that is,
{\vec{v}}(t,p)={\partial\Phi\over\partial t}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
when
p={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
.
Its differential
d{\vec{v}}
is given in (
2.8
).

At
t
, an observer chooses a unit of measurement (foot, metre…)
and builds the associated Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
, cf. §
B.2
.
(We loose the objective point of view here).
And the same
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
is used at all
t
.

C.1
The symmetric and antisymmetric parts of
d{\vec{v}}

With the imposed chosen Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
,
we can consider the transposed endomorphism
d{\vec{v}}_{t}(p)^{T}_{g}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}d{\vec{v}}_{t}(p)^{T}\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})
, which is defined by, for all
{{\vec{w}}_{1}},{{\vec{w}}_{2}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
vectors at
p
,

(d{\vec{v}}_{t}(p)^{T}.{{\vec{w}}_{1}},{{\vec{w}}_{2}})_{g}=({{\vec{w}}_{1}},d{\vec{v}}_{t}(p).{{\vec{w}}_{2}})_{g}

(C.1)

cf. §
A.11
.
We have thus defined

d{\vec{v}}_{t}^{T}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\Omega_{t}}&\rightarrow{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})\cr p&\rightarrow d{\vec{v}}_{t}^{T}(p):=d{\vec{v}}_{t}(p)^{T}\cr}}\,\right.

(C.2)

Other usual notations (definitions):

d{\vec{v}}_{t}(p)^{T}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}d{\vec{v}}(t,p)^{T}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}d{\vec{v}}^{T}(t,p)
.

Definition C.1

The (Eulerian) rate of deformation tensor, or stretching tensor, is the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-symmetric part of
d{\vec{v}}
:

{\cal D}={d{\vec{v}}+d{\vec{v}}^{T}\over 2},\quad\hbox{i{.}e{.},}\quad\forall(t,p)\in\bigcup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}(\{t\}\times{\Omega_{t}}),\quad{\cal D}(t,p)={d{\vec{v}}(t,p)+d{\vec{v}}(t,p)^{T}\over 2}.

(C.3)

The (Eulerian) spin tensor is the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-antisymmetric part of
d{\vec{v}}
:

\Omega={d{\vec{v}}-d{\vec{v}}^{T}\over 2},\quad\hbox{i{.}e{.},}\quad\forall(t,p)\in\bigcup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}(\{t\}\times{\Omega_{t}}),\quad\Omega(t,p)={d{\vec{v}}(t,p)-d{\vec{v}}(t,p)^{T}\over 2}.

(C.4)

(So
d{\vec{v}}={\cal D}+\Omega
with
{\cal D}
the rate of deformation tensor and
\Omega={\vec{\omega}}\wedge
a rotation times a dilation, see the following.)

NB: The same notation is used for the set of points
\Omega_{t}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({\Omega_{t_{0}}})\subset{\mathbb{R}^{n}}

and for the spin tensor
\Omega_{t}={d{\vec{v}}_{t}-d{\vec{v}}_{t}^{T}\over 2}
: The context removes ambiguities.

C.2
Quantification with a basis

With a basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
, (
C.1
) gives

[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[d{\vec{v}}^{T}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[d{\vec{v}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}.[g]_{|{\vec{e}}},\quad\hbox{and}\quad[d{\vec{v}}^{T}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{-1}.[d{\vec{v}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}.[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}.

(C.5)

In particular, if
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-orthonormal basis, then

[d{\vec{v}}^{T}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[d{\vec{v}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}
(orthonormal basis case).
Thus for the endomorphisms
{\cal D}
and
\Omega
,
and with the above Euclidean framework and its Euclidean orthonormal basis, we have

{\cal D}.{\vec{e}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\cal D}_{ij}{\vec{e}}_{i}
and
\Omega.{\vec{e}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\Omega_{ij}{\vec{e}}_{i}
with

{\cal D}_{ij}={1\over 2}({\partial v_{i}\over\partial x_{j}}+{\partial v_{j}\over\partial x_{i}})
and

\Omega_{ij}={1\over 2}({\partial v_{i}\over\partial x_{j}}-{\partial v_{j}\over\partial x_{i}})
, that is,

[{\cal D}]_{|{\vec{e}}}={[d{\vec{v}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}+[d{\vec{v}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}\over 2}\quad\hbox{and}\quad[\Omega]_{|{\vec{e}}}={[d{\vec{v}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}-[d{\vec{v}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}\over 2}\quad\hbox{(Euclidean framework)}.

(C.6)

Duality notations:

{\cal D}.{\vec{e}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\cal D}^{i}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,
{\cal D}^{i}_{j}={1\over 2}({\partial v^{i}\over\partial x^{j}}+{\partial v^{j}\over\partial x^{i}})

and

\Omega.{\vec{e}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\Omega^{i}{}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,
\Omega^{i}{}_{j}={1\over 2}({\partial v^{i}\over\partial x^{j}}-{\partial v^{j}\over\partial x^{i}})
,
so with
{\cal D}^{i}_{j}={\cal D}^{j}_{i}
and
\Omega^{i}{}_{j}=-\Omega^{j}{}_{i}
.

Appendix D
Interpretation of the rate of deformation tensor

We are interested in the evolution of the deformation gradient
F(t):={F^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t)
along the trajectory of a particle
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
which was at
{p_{t_{0}}}
at
{t_{0}}
. So:

Let
{\vec{A}}={\vec{a}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})
and
{\vec{B}}={\vec{b}}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})
be vectors at
{t_{0}}
at
{p_{t_{0}}}
in
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
,
and consider their push-forwards by the flow
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
(the transported vectors), i.e. the vectors at
t
at
p(t)={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t)
given by

{\vec{a}}(t,p(t)):=F(t).{\vec{A}}\quad\hbox{and}\quad{\vec{b}}(t,p(t)):=F(t).{\vec{B}}.

(D.1)

see (
4.3
) and figure
4.1
.
They define the function

({\vec{a}},{\vec{b}})_{g}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\hbox{\large$\cal C$}&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr(t,{p_{t}})&\rightarrow({\vec{a}},{\vec{b}})_{g}(t,{p_{t}}):=({\vec{a}}(t,{p_{t}}),{\vec{b}}(t,{p_{t}}))_{g}.\crcr}}\,\right.

(D.2)

Proposition D.1

The rate of deformation tensor
{\cal D}={d{\vec{v}}+d{\vec{v}}^{T}\over 2}
gives (half) the evolution rate between two vectors deformed by the flow, that is, along trajectories,

{D({\vec{a}},{\vec{b}})_{g}\over Dt}=2({\cal D}.{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}})_{g}.

(D.3)

Proof.
f(t):=({\vec{a}}(t,p(t)),{\vec{b}}(t,p(t)))_{g}=(F(t).{\vec{A}},F(t).{\vec{B}})_{g}
gives

f^{\prime}(t)=(F^{\prime}(t).{\vec{A}},F(t).{\vec{B}})_{g}+(F(t).{\vec{A}},F^{\prime}(t).{\vec{B}})_{g}.

(D.4)

And
F^{\prime}(t)=d{\vec{v}}(t,p(t)).F(t)
, cf. (
3.33
). Thus, with
{\vec{a}}(t,p(t))=F(t).{\vec{A}}
and
{\vec{b}}(t,p(t))=F(t).{\vec{B}}
,

\displaystyle{f^{\prime}(t)=}

\displaystyle{{}(d{\vec{v}}(t,p(t)).F(t).{\vec{A}},F(t).{\vec{B}})_{g}+(F(t).{\vec{A}},d{\vec{v}}(t,p(t)).F(t).{\vec{B}})_{g}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}(d{\vec{v}}(t,p(t)).{\vec{a}}(t,p(t)),{\vec{b}}(t,p(t)))_{g}+({\vec{a}}(t,p(t)),d{\vec{v}}(t,p(t)).{\vec{b}}(t,p(t)))_{g}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}((d{\vec{v}}(t,p(t))+d{\vec{v}}(t,p(t))^{T}).{\vec{a}}(t,p(t)),{\vec{b}}(t,p(t)))_{g},}

(D.5)

i.e. (
D.3
), since
f(t)=({\vec{a}},{\vec{b}})_{g}(t,p(t))
gives
f^{\prime}(t)={D({\vec{a}},{\vec{b}})_{g}\over Dt}(t,p(t))
.

Appendix E
Rigid body motions and the spin tensor

Choose a Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
(required to characterize a rigid body motion).

Result: A rigid body motion is a motion whose Eulerian velocity satisfies
d{\vec{v}}+d{\vec{v}}^{T}=0
, i.e.,
{\cal D}=0

(Eulerian approach independent of any initial time
{t_{0}}
chosen by some observer).

But the usual classical introduction to rigid body motion relies on some initial time
{t_{0}}
(Lagrangian approach). So, to begin with, let us do it with the Lagrangian approach.
Recall: T the first order Taylor expansion of
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
in the vicinity of a
{p_{t_{0}}}\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
is

{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({q_{t_{0}}})={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})+{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).\overrightarrow{{p_{t_{0}}}{q_{t_{0}}}}+o(\overrightarrow{{p_{t_{0}}}{q_{t_{0}}}}).

(E.1)

E.1
Affine motions and rigid body motions

E.1.1
Affine motions

Definition E.1

{\Phi^{t_{0}}}
is an affine motion (understood “affine motion in space”) iff
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
is an “affine motion”,
i.e. iff
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
is a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism (in space), and
(
E.1
) reads, for all
{p_{t_{0}}},{q_{t_{0}}}\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
and all
t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]
,

{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({q_{t_{0}}})={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})+{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).\overrightarrow{{p_{t_{0}}}{q_{t_{0}}}}.

(E.2)

Marsden–Hughes notations:
\Phi(Q)=\Phi(P)+F(P).\overrightarrow{PQ}
.

Proposition E.2

and definition.

If
{\Phi^{t_{0}}}
is an affine motion, then
{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})
is independent of
{p_{t_{0}}}
, i.e.,
for all
t\in]t_{1},t_{2}[
and all
{p_{t_{0}}}\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
and all
{q_{t_{0}}}\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
,

{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})={F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({q_{t_{0}}}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}.

(E.3)

And then
d{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})=0
, i.e.
d^{2}{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})=0
. And for all
t\in]t_{1},t_{2}[
,
\Phi^{t}
is an affine motion: For all
\tau\in]t_{1},t_{2}[
and all
{p_{t}},{q_{t}}\in{\Omega_{t}}
,

{\Phi^{t}_{\tau}}({q_{t}})={\Phi^{t}_{\tau}}({p_{t}})+F^{t}_{\tau}.\overrightarrow{{p_{t}}{q_{t}}}.

(E.4)

And
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
is said to be an affine motion (understood “affine motion in space”).

Proof.
{q_{t_{0}}}={p_{t_{0}}}+\overrightarrow{{p_{t_{0}}}{q_{t_{0}}}}
gives

{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({q_{t_{0}}})={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}+\overrightarrow{{p_{t_{0}}}{q_{t_{0}}}})={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})+d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).\overrightarrow{{p_{t_{0}}}{q_{t_{0}}}}
,
and, similarly,

{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({q_{t_{0}}}+\overrightarrow{{q_{t_{0}}}{p_{t_{0}}}})={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({q_{t_{0}}})+d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({q_{t_{0}}}).\overrightarrow{{q_{t_{0}}}{p_{t_{0}}}}
.
Thus (addition)

{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({q_{t_{0}}})+{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})+{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({q_{t_{0}}})+(d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})-d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({q_{t_{0}}})).\overrightarrow{{p_{t_{0}}}{q_{t_{0}}}}
,
thus
(d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})-d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({q_{t_{0}}})).\overrightarrow{{p_{t_{0}}}{q_{t_{0}}}}=0
, true for all
{p_{t_{0}}},{q_{t_{0}}}
,
thus
d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})-d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({q_{t_{0}}})=0
, i.e. (
E.3
).

Thus
d^{2}{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{u}}_{t_{0}}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}+h{\vec{u}}_{t_{0}})-d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})\over h}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}-d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}\over h}=0

for all
{p_{t_{0}}}
and all
{\vec{u}}_{t_{0}}
, thus
d^{2}{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})=0
for all
{p_{t_{0}}}
, thus
d^{2}{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}=0
.

And (
5.17
) gives

({\Phi^{t}_{\tau}}\circ{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})({p_{t_{0}}})=\Phi^{t_{0}}_{\tau}({p_{t_{0}}})
,
thus, with
{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})
, we get
d{\Phi^{t}_{\tau}}({p_{t}}).d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})=d\Phi^{t_{0}}_{\tau}({p_{t_{0}}})
,
thus
d{\Phi^{t}_{\tau}}({p_{t}})=d\Phi^{t_{0}}_{\tau}({p_{t_{0}}}).d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})^{-1}
,
and (
E.2
) gives

d{\Phi^{t}_{\tau}}({p_{t}})=d\Phi^{t_{0}}_{\tau}.d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}^{-1}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}d{\Phi^{t}_{\tau}}\quad(\hbox{independent of\penalty\ ${p_{t}}$}),

(E.5)

thus (
E.4
).

Corollary E.3

If
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
is affine then,
{\vec{v}}_{t}
is affine for all
t
, and
{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}
is affine for all
{t_{0}},t
, i.e.,
for all
{p_{t}}\in{\Omega_{t}}
we have
d{\vec{v}}_{t}({p_{t}})=d{\vec{v}}_{t}
(independent of
{p_{t}}
), and for all
{p_{t_{0}}}\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
we have
d{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}d{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}
(independent of
{p_{t_{0}}}
): For all
{q_{t}}\in{\Omega_{t}}
and all
{q_{t_{0}}}\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
,

\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\bullet\;&{\vec{v}}_{t}({q_{t}})={\vec{v}}_{t}({p_{t}})+d{\vec{v}}_{t}.\overrightarrow{{p_{t}}{q_{t}}},\cr\bullet\;&{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({q_{t_{0}}})={{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})+d{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}.\overrightarrow{{p_{t_{0}}}{q_{t_{0}}}}.\cr}}\,\right.

(E.6)

Proof.
(
E.2
) gives
{\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{q_{t_{0}}})={\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})+{F^{t_{0}}}(t).\overrightarrow{{p_{t_{0}}}{q_{t_{0}}}}
,
and the derivation in time gives (
E.6
)
2
,
then (
E.6
)
1
thanks to
{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})
,
{q_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({q_{t_{0}}})
and
\overrightarrow{{p_{t_{0}}}{q_{t_{0}}}}=({F^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{-1}.\overrightarrow{{p_{t}}{q_{t}}}
, cf. (
E.2
).

Example E.4

In
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, with a basis
({\vec{E}}_{1},{\vec{E}}_{2})
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
and a basis
({\vec{e}}_{1},{\vec{e}}_{2})\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
, then
{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}
given by
[{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}]_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{1+t&2t^{2}\cr 3t^{3}&e^{t}}

derives from the affine motion

[\overrightarrow{{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}){\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({q_{t_{0}}})}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{1+t&2t^{2}\cr 3t^{3}&e^{t}}.[\overrightarrow{{p_{t_{0}}}{q_{t_{0}}}}]_{|{\vec{E}}}
.

E.1.2
Rigid body motion

A Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
is chosen, the same at all time
t
. Let
\Phi:={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
and
F:={F^{t_{0}}_{t}}
.
Recall: If
P\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
and
p=\Phi(P)
(
\in{\Omega_{t}}
) then the transposed of the linear map
F(P)\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})
relative to
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
is the linear map
F^{T}(p):=F(P)^{T}\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}})
defined by

F^{T}(p):=F(P)^{T}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}&\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}\cr{\vec{w}}_{p}&\rightarrow F^{T}(p).{\vec{w}}_{p}\quad\hbox{s{.}t{.}}\quad(F^{T}(p).{\vec{w}}_{p},{\vec{U}}_{P})_{g}=({\vec{w}}_{p},F(P).{\vec{U}}_{P})_{g},\;\;\forall{\vec{U}}_{P}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}.\cr}}\,\right.

(E.7)

We have thus defined the function
F^{T}:{\Omega_{t}}\rightarrow{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}})
.

Particular case: For an affine motion, since
F
is independent of
P
, we get
F^{T}
is independent of
p
.

Definition E.5

A rigid body motion is an affine motion
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
such that, for all
{t_{0}},t\in\mathbb{R}
,
P\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
,
{\vec{U}}_{P},{\vec{W}}_{P}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
, and with
p={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
,

(F.{\vec{U}}_{P},F.{\vec{W}}_{P})_{g}=({\vec{U}}_{P},{\vec{W}}_{P})_{g},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad(F^{T}.F.{\vec{U}}_{P},{\vec{W}}_{P})_{g}=({\vec{U}}_{P},{\vec{W}}_{P})_{g},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle F^{T}.F=I$}.

(E.8)
