(Angles and lengths are unchanged.)

In other words, with the Cauchy strain tensor
C\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}})
defined by
C=F^{T}.F
,
the motion is rigid iff it is affine and

\framebox{$\displaystyle C=I$},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle F^{-1}=F^{T}$}.

(E.9)

Proposition E.6

If
{\Phi^{t_{0}}}
is a rigid body motion, if
({\vec{A}}_{i})
is a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Euclidean basis in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
,
if
P\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
, if
t\in[{t_{0}},T]
and
p={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
,
and if
{\vec{a}}_{i}(t,p)={F^{t_{0}}}(t,P).{\vec{A}}_{i}
for all
i
,
then
{\vec{a}}_{i}(t,p){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{a}}_{i,t}
is independent of
p
, and
({\vec{a}}_{i,t})
is a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Euclidean basis with the same orientation than
({\vec{A}}_{i})
for all
t
.

Proof.
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
is affine, thus, for all
t,P
,
{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)={F^{t_{0}}_{t}}
(independent of
P
),
thus
{\vec{a}}_{i,t}(p)={F^{t_{0}}_{t}}.{\vec{A}}_{i}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
is independent of
p
, this at all
t
. Let
t
be fixed and
{\vec{a}}_{i,t}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{a}}_{i}
(
=F.{\vec{A}}_{j}
).
We get

({\vec{a}}_{i},{\vec{a}}_{j})_{g}=(F.{\vec{A}}_{i},F.{\vec{A}}_{j})_{g}=(F^{T}.F.{\vec{A}}_{i},{\vec{A}}_{j})_{g}=(I.{\vec{A}}_{i},{\vec{A}}_{j})_{g}=({\vec{A}}_{i},{\vec{A}}_{j})_{g}=\delta_{ij}

for all
i,j
, thus
({\vec{a}}_{i})
is
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-orthonormal basis.
And

\det({\vec{a}}_{1},...,{\vec{a}}_{n})=\det(F.{\vec{A}}_{1},...,F.{\vec{A}}_{n})=\det(F)\det({\vec{A}}_{1},...,{\vec{A}}_{n})=\det(F)
since
({\vec{A}}_{i})
is a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
- orthonormal basis.
And,
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
being a diffeomorphism,

t\rightarrow\det({F^{t_{0}}_{t}})
is continuous, does not vanish, moreover with
\det(F^{t_{0}}_{t_{0}})=\det(I)=1>0
; Thus
\det({F^{t_{0}}_{t}})>0
for all
t
,
hence
\det({\vec{a}}_{1},...,{\vec{a}}_{n})>0
:
The bases have the same orientation.

Example E.7

In
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, a rigid body motion is given by

{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}=\pmatrix{\cos(\theta(t))&-\sin(\theta(t))\cr\sin(\theta(t))&\cos(\theta(t))}

with
\theta
a regular function s.t.
\theta({t_{0}})=0
.

Exercice E.8

Let
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
be a rigid body motion. Prove

(F^{T})^{\prime}(t)=(F^{\prime}(t))^{T},\quad\hbox{and}\quad\hbox{$F^{T}.F^{\prime}$ is antisymmetric}.

(E.10)

Answer
. Let
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
p(t)={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
,
{\vec{U}},{\vec{W}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
and
{\vec{w}}(t,p(t))=F(t).{\vec{W}}
.
And recall that the function
F^{T}:t\rightarrow F^{T}(t)
is defined (as usual) by
F^{T}(t):=(F(t))^{T}
.
We have

(F(t)^{T}.{\vec{w}}(t,p(t)),{\vec{U}})_{g}=({\vec{w}}(t,p(t)),F(t).{\vec{U}})_{g}
. Thus

((F^{T})^{\prime}(t).{\vec{w}}(t,p(t))+F^{T}(t).{D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}(t,p(t)),{\vec{U}})_{g}=({D{\vec{w}}\over Dt}(t,p(t)),F(t).{\vec{U}})_{g}+({\vec{w}}(t,p(t)),F^{\prime}(t).{\vec{U}})_{g}
, which simplifies into

((F^{T})^{\prime}(t).{\vec{w}}(t,p(t)),{\vec{U}})_{g}=({\vec{w}}(t,p(t)),F^{\prime}(t).{\vec{U}})_{g}=((F^{\prime}(t))^{T}.{\vec{w}}(t,p(t)),{\vec{U}})_{g}
, thus
(F^{T})^{\prime}(t)=(F^{\prime}(t))^{T}
.

And (
E.8
) reads
F^{T}(t).F(t)=I_{t_{0}}
, thus

(F^{T})^{\prime}(t).F(t)+F^{T}(t).F^{\prime}(t)=0
, thus

(F^{\prime})^{T}(t).F(t)+F^{T}(t).F^{\prime}(t)=0
, thus
F^{T}.F^{\prime}
is antisymmetric.

E.1.3
Alternative definition of a rigid body motion:
d{\vec{v}}+d{\vec{v}}^{T}=0

The stretching tensor
{\cal D}_{t}={d{\vec{v}}_{t}+d{\vec{v}}_{t}^{T}\over 2}
and the spin tensor
{\Omega_{t}}={d{\vec{v}}_{t}-d{\vec{v}}_{t}^{T}\over 2}
have been defined in (
C.3
)-(
C.4
).

Proposition E.9

If
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
is a rigid body motion, cf. (
E.8
), then the endomorphism
d{\vec{v}}_{t}\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t}}})
is antisymmetric at all
t
:

d{\vec{v}}_{t}=\Omega_{t},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad{\cal D}_{t}=0.

(E.11)

Conversely, if
d{\vec{v}}_{t}+d{\vec{v}}_{t}^{T}=0
at all
t
, then
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
is a rigid body motion (here no initial time is required).

So the relation <<
d{\vec{v}}_{t}+d{\vec{v}}_{t}^{T}=0
for all
t
>> gives an equivalent definition to the definition
E.5
.

Proof.
Let
F(t):={F^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t)
and
F^{T}(t):=F(t)^{T}
and
V(t):={\vec{V}^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t)=({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}})^{\prime}(t)={\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}})
(the Lagrangian and Eulerian velocities). (
E.8
) gives

\displaystyle(F.F^{T})^{\prime}(t)=0=F^{\prime}(t).F(t)^{T}+F(t).(F^{T})^{\prime}(t)\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqFtpT})}F^{\prime}(t).F(t)^{T}+(F^{\prime}(t).F(t)^{T})^{T}=dV(t).F(t)^{-1}+(dV(t).F(t)^{-1})^{T}\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqvVvv})}d{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}})+d{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}})^{T}
. Thus (
E.11
).

Conversely, suppose
d{\vec{v}}+d{\vec{v}}^{T}=0
. Then (
D.3
) gives
{D({\vec{a}},{\vec{b}})_{g}\over Dt}=0
,
thus
({\vec{a}},{\vec{b}})_{g}(t,{p_{t}})=({\vec{a}},{\vec{b}})_{g}({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})
for all
t,{t_{0}}
and all
{p_{t_{0}}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t}})
,
i.e.
({F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{A}},{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{B}})_{g}=({\vec{A}},{\vec{B}})_{g}
for all
t,{t_{0}}
, all
{p_{t_{0}}}
and all
{\vec{A}},{\vec{B}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
:
Thus
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
is a rigid body motion, cf (
E.8
).

E.2
Representation of the spin tensor
\Omega
: vectors, and pseudo-vectors

We are dealing here with concepts that are sometimes misunderstood.
Framework:
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}=\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

E.2.1
Reminder

\bullet
The determinant
\det_{|{\vec{e}}}
associated with a basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
is the alternating multilinear form defined by

\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{e}}_{1},{\vec{e}}_{2},{\vec{e}}_{3})=1
; The algebraic volume (or signed volume) limited by three vectors
{\vec{u}}_{1},{\vec{u}}_{2},{\vec{u}}_{3}

is
\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{u}}_{1},{\vec{u}}_{2},{\vec{u}}_{3})
; And the (positive) volume is
|\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{u}}_{1},{\vec{u}}_{2},{\vec{u}}_{3})|
, see §
K
.

\bullet
Let
A
and
B
be two observers (e.g.
A
=English and
B
=French),
let
({\vec{a}}_{i})
be a Euclidean basis chosen by
A
(e.g. based on the foot),
let
({\vec{b}}_{i})
be a Euclidean basis chosen by
B
(e.g. based on the metre), see §
B.1
.
Let
\lambda=||{\vec{b}}_{1}||_{a}>0
(change of unit of length coefficient). The relation between the determinants is:

\det_{|{\vec{a}}}=\pm\lambda^{3}\,\det_{|{\vec{b}}}\quad\hbox{with}\quad\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&+\hbox{ if }\det_{|{\vec{a}}}({\vec{b}}_{1},{\vec{b}}_{2},{\vec{b}}_{3})>0\quad\hbox{(i{.}e{.}\ if the bases have the same orientation)},\cr&-\hbox{ if }\det_{|{\vec{a}}}({\vec{b}}_{1},{\vec{b}}_{2},{\vec{b}}_{3})<0\quad\hbox{(i{.}e{.}\ if the bases have opposite orientation)}.\cr}}\,\right.

(E.12)

In particular, if
A
and
B
use the same unit of length
(or if
A
uses two
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Euclidean basis
({\vec{a}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
), then
\lambda=1
and
\det_{|{\vec{a}}}=\pm\det_{|{\vec{b}}}
.

\bullet

With an imposed Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
: An endomorphism
L
is
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-antisymmetric iff

\forall{\vec{u}},{\vec{v}},\;\;(L.{\vec{u}},{\vec{v}})_{g}+({\vec{u}},L.{\vec{v}})_{g}=0,\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad L^{T}=-L.

(E.13)

E.2.2
Definition of the vector product (cross product)

Let
({\vec{e}}_{i})
be a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-orthonormal basis,
let
{\vec{u}},{\vec{v}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}
, and let
\ell_{{\vec{e}},{\vec{u}},{\vec{v}}}\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}},\mathbb{R})
be the linear form defined by

\ell_{{\vec{e}},{\vec{u}},{\vec{v}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr{\vec{z}}&\rightarrow\ell_{{\vec{e}},{\vec{u}},{\vec{v}}}({\vec{z}}):=\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{u}},{\vec{v}},{\vec{z}})\crcr}}\,\right.

(E.14)

(the algebraic volume of the parallelepiped limited by
{\vec{u}},{\vec{v}},{\vec{z}}
in the Euclidean chosen unit).

Definition E.10

The vector product, or cross product,
{\vec{u}}\wedge_{e}{\vec{v}}
of two vectors
{\vec{u}}
and
{\vec{v}}

is the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Riesz representation vector of
\ell_{{\vec{e}},{\vec{u}},{\vec{v}}}
,
that is,
{\vec{u}}\wedge_{e}{\vec{v}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}
is characterized by

\ell_{{\vec{e}},{\vec{u}},{\vec{v}}}({\vec{z}})=({\vec{u}}\wedge_{e}{\vec{v}},{\vec{z}})_{g}
for all
{\vec{z}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}
, cf. (
F.2
), i.e.

\forall{\vec{z}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}},\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle({\vec{u}}\wedge_{e}{\vec{v}},{\vec{z}})_{g}=\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{u}},{\vec{v}},{\vec{z}})$}.

(E.15)

NB:
{\vec{u}}\wedge_{e}{\vec{v}}
depends on
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
since we need a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Euclidean basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
(and depends on the orientation of
({\vec{e}}_{i})
.

We have thus defined the bilinear cross product operator

\wedge_{e}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}\times{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}&\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}\cr({\vec{u}},{\vec{v}})&\rightarrow\wedge_{e}({\vec{u}},{\vec{v}}):={\vec{u}}\wedge_{e}{\vec{v}}.\crcr}}\,\right.

(E.16)

(The bilinearity is trivial thanks to the multilinearity of the determinant.)
If one Euclidean basis is imposed by one observer to all the other observers, then

{\vec{u}}\wedge_{e}{\vec{v}}
is written
{\vec{u}}\wedge{\vec{v}}
(non objective).

E.2.3
Calculation of the vector product

{\vec{u}}=\sum_{i=1}^{3}u_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,
{\vec{v}}=\sum_{i=1}^{3}v_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
and (
E.15
) give

({\vec{u}}\wedge_{e}{\vec{v}},{\vec{e}}_{1})_{g}=\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{u}},{\vec{v}},{\vec{e}}_{1})=\det\pmatrix{u_{1}&v_{1}&1\cr u_{2}&v_{2}&0\cr u_{3}&v_{3}&0}=\det\pmatrix{u_{2}&v_{2}\cr u_{3}&v_{3}}=u_{2}v_{3}-u_{3}v_{2}.

(E.17)

Similar calculation for
({\vec{u}}\wedge_{e}{\vec{v}},{\vec{e}}_{2})_{e}
and
({\vec{u}}\wedge_{e}{\vec{v}},{\vec{e}}_{3})_{e}
, thus

{\vec{u}}\wedge_{e}{\vec{v}}=\sum_{i=1}^{3}(u_{i+1}v_{i+2}-u_{i+2}v_{i+1})\,{\vec{e}}_{i},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad[{\vec{u}}\wedge_{e}{\vec{v}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{u_{2}v_{3}-u_{3}v_{2}\cr u_{3}v_{1}-u_{1}v_{3}\cr u_{1}v_{2}-u_{2}v_{1}\cr}.

(E.18)

with the generic notation
w_{4}:=w_{1}
and
w_{5}=w_{2}
.
(In particular
{\vec{e}}_{i}\wedge_{e}{\vec{e}}_{i+1}={\vec{e}}_{i+2}
.)

Proposition E.11

1-
{\vec{u}}\wedge_{e}{\vec{v}}=-{\vec{v}}\wedge_{e}{\vec{u}}
.

2-
{\vec{u}}\parallel{\vec{v}}
iff
{\vec{u}}\wedge_{e}{\vec{v}}=0
.

3- If
{\vec{u}}
and
{\vec{v}}
are independent then

{\vec{u}}\wedge_{e}{\vec{v}}
is orthogonal to the linear space
{\rm Vect}\{{\vec{u}},{\vec{v}}\}
generated by
{\vec{u}}
and
{\vec{v}}
.

4-
{\vec{u}}\wedge_{e}{\vec{v}}
depends on the unit of measurement and on the orientation of
({\vec{e}}_{i})
:
If
(\cdot,\cdot)_{a}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{b}
are two Euclidean dot products, let
\lambda>0
such that
(\cdot,\cdot)_{a}=\lambda^{2}(\cdot,\cdot)_{b}
, and then

{\vec{u}}\wedge_{a}{\vec{v}}=\pm\lambda{\vec{u}}\wedge_{b}{\vec{v}}.

(E.19)

Proof.
1-
\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{u}},{\vec{v}},{\vec{z}})=-\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{v}},{\vec{u}},{\vec{z}})
(since
\det_{|{\vec{e}}}
is alternated).

2- If
{\vec{u}}\parallel{\vec{v}}
then

\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{u}},{\vec{v}},{\vec{z}})=0=({\vec{u}}\wedge_{e}{\vec{v}},{\vec{z}})_{e}
, so
{\vec{u}}\wedge_{e}{\vec{v}}\perp_{g}{\vec{z}}
, for all
{\vec{z}}
.
And if
{\vec{u}}\wedge_{e}{\vec{v}}=0
then (
E.18
) gives
{\vec{u}}\parallel{\vec{v}}
.

3- If
{\vec{z}}\in{\rm Vect}\{{\vec{u}},{\vec{v}}\}

then
\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{u}},{\vec{v}},{\vec{z}})=0=({\vec{u}}\wedge_{e}{\vec{v}},{\vec{z}})_{g}
thus
{\vec{u}}\wedge_{e}{\vec{v}}\perp_{g}{\vec{z}}
.

4-
\displaystyle({\vec{u}}\wedge_{a}{\vec{v}},{\vec{z}})_{a}\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqw1})}\det_{|{\vec{a}}}({\vec{u}},{\vec{v}},{\vec{z}})\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdetlambda})}\pm\lambda^{3}\det_{|{\vec{b}}}({\vec{u}},{\vec{v}},{\vec{z}})\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqw1})}\pm\lambda^{3}({\vec{u}}\wedge_{b}{\vec{v}},{\vec{z}})_{b}=\pm\lambda^{3}{1\over\lambda^{2}}({\vec{u}}\wedge_{b}{\vec{v}},{\vec{z}})_{a}
,
true for all
{\vec{z}}
, thus (
E.19
).

Exercice E.12

Prove that
{\vec{u}}\wedge_{e}{\vec{v}}
is a contravariant vector.

Answer
. It is a vector (Riesz representation vector) in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}
, so it is contravariant;
Or calculation: It satisfies the contravariance change of basis formula, see (
F.17
).

E.2.4
Antisymmetric endomorphism represented by a vector

Proposition E.13

Let
({\vec{e}}_{i})
be a chosen
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Euclidean basis.
If an endomorphism
\Omega\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}})
is
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-antisymmetric then there exists a unique vector
{\vec{\omega}}_{e}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}
s.t., for all
{\vec{y}},{\vec{z}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}
,

(\Omega.{\vec{y}},{\vec{z}})_{g}=\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{\omega}}_{e},{\vec{y}},{\vec{z}}),

(E.20)

i.e., there exists a unique vector
{\vec{\omega}}_{e}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}
s.t., for all
{\vec{y}},{\vec{z}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}
,

\framebox{$\displaystyle\Omega.{\vec{y}}={\vec{\omega}}_{e}\wedge_{e}{\vec{y}}$},

(E.21)

And

[\Omega]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{0&-c&b\cr c&0&-a\cr-b&a&0}\quad\hbox{iff}\quad[{\vec{\omega}}_{e}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{a\cr b\cr c}.

(E.22)

In particular
\Omega.{\vec{\omega}}_{e}=\vec{0}
(
={\vec{\omega}}_{e}\wedge_{e}{\vec{\omega}}_{e}
),
i.e.
{\vec{\omega}}_{e}
is an eigenvector associated with the eigenvalue
0
.

Proof.
\Omega
is antisymmetric, thus
[\Omega]_{|{\vec{e}}}
is given as in (
E.22
).
In particular
[\Omega.{\vec{e}}_{1}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[\Omega]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{e}}_{1}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{0\cr c\cr-b}
.
Calculation of the components of
{\vec{\omega}}_{e}
if it exists:
Let
{\vec{\omega}}=\omega_{1}{\vec{e}}_{1}+\omega_{2}{\vec{e}}_{2}+\omega_{3}{\vec{e}}_{3}
;
thus
[{\vec{\omega}}\wedge{\vec{e}}_{1}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{0\cr\omega_{3}\cr-\omega_{2}}
, cf. (
E.18
),
thus
\omega_{3}=c
and
\omega_{2}=b
; Idem with
{\vec{e}}_{2}
so that
\omega_{1}=a
.
Thus if it exists
{\vec{\omega}}
is unique. And
{\vec{\omega}}_{e}
given in (
E.22
) satisfies (
E.21
): It exists.

Proposition E.14

Let
(\cdot,\cdot)_{a}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{b}
be two Euclidean dot products (e.g. in foot and metre),
let
({\vec{a}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
be Euclidean associated bases,
let
||{\vec{b}}_{1}||_{a}=\lambda
(change of unit coefficient), so
(\cdot,\cdot)_{a}=\lambda_{2}(\cdot,\cdot)_{b}
).
Suppose
[\Omega]_{|{\vec{a}}}=\pmatrix{0&-c&b\cr c&0&-a\cr-b&a&0}
, thus

[{\vec{\omega}}_{a}]_{|{\vec{a}}}=\pmatrix{a\cr b\cr c}
, cf. (
E.22
). Then
(change of representation vector for
\Omega
):

\displaystyle{\bullet\;}

\displaystyle{{}\hbox{If $({\vec{b}}_{i})$ and $({\vec{a}}_{i})$ have the same orientation, then}\quad{\vec{\omega}}_{b}=\lambda{\vec{\omega}}_{a},}

\displaystyle{\bullet\;}

\displaystyle{{}\hbox{If $({\vec{b}}_{i})$ and $({\vec{a}}_{i})$ have opposite orientation, then}\quad{\vec{\omega}}_{b}=-\lambda{\vec{\omega}}_{a},}

(E.23)

E.g., if
{\vec{b}}_{i}=\lambda{\vec{a}}_{i}
for all
i
(change of unit, same orientation) then

{\vec{\omega}}_{b}=\lambda{\vec{\omega}}_{a}
, and
if
{\vec{b}}_{1}=-\lambda{\vec{a}}_{1}
,
{\vec{b}}_{2}=\lambda{\vec{a}}_{2}
,
{\vec{b}}_{3}=\lambda{\vec{a}}_{3}
(change of unit, opposite orientation) then
{\vec{\omega}}_{b}=-\lambda{\vec{\omega}}_{a}
.

NB:

The formula
{\vec{\omega}}_{b}=\pm\lambda{\vec{\omega}}_{a}
is a change of vector formula,

not
a change of basis formula.

Proof.
Apply (
E.19
).

Interpretation of
{\vec{\omega}}_{e}
:

Suppose
[\Omega]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\alpha\pmatrix{0&-1&0\cr 1&0&0\cr 0&0&0}
.
So
\Omega
is the rotation with angle
{\pi\over 2}
in the horizontal plane
composed with the dilation with ratio
\alpha
.
And
[{\vec{\omega}}_{e}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\alpha\pmatrix{0\cr 0\cr 1}=\alpha\,{\vec{e}}_{3}
is orthogonal to the horizontal plane and gives the rotation axis and the dilation coefficient.

Exercice E.15

Let
\Omega
s.t.
[\Omega]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{0&-c&b\cr c&0&-a\cr-b&a&0}

(see (
E.22
)).
Find a direct orthonormal basis
({\vec{b}}_{i})
(relative to
({\vec{e}}_{i})
) s.t.
[\Omega]_{|{\vec{b}}}=\sqrt{a^{2}{+}b^{2}{+}c^{2}}\pmatrix{0&-1&0\cr 1&0&0\cr 0&0&0}
.

Answer
. Let
{\vec{b}}_{3}={{\vec{\omega}}_{e}\over||{\vec{\omega}}_{e}||_{e}}
, that is,

[{\vec{b}}_{3}]_{|{\vec{e}}}={1\over\sqrt{a^{2}{+}b^{2}{+}c^{2}}}\pmatrix{a\cr b\cr c}
.
Then choose
{\vec{b}}_{1}\perp{\vec{b}}_{3}
, e.g.
[{\vec{b}}_{1}]_{|{\vec{e}}}={1\over\sqrt{a^{2}{+}b^{2}}}\pmatrix{-b\cr a\cr 0}
.
Then choose
{\vec{b}}_{2}={\vec{b}}_{3}\wedge_{e}{\vec{b}}_{1}
,
that is,
[{\vec{b}}_{2}]_{|{\vec{e}}}={1\over\sqrt{a^{2}{+}b^{2}}}{1\over\sqrt{a^{2}{+}b^{2}{+}c^{2}}}\pmatrix{-ac\cr-bc\cr a^{2}+b^{2}}
.
Thus
({\vec{b}}_{i})
is a direct orthonormal basis, and the transition matrix from
({\vec{e}}_{i})
to
({\vec{b}}_{i})

is
P=\pmatrix{[{\vec{b}}_{1}]_{|{\vec{e}}}&[{\vec{b}}_{2}]_{|{\vec{e}}}&[{\vec{b}}_{3}]_{|{\vec{e}}}}
.
And
[\Omega]_{|{\vec{b}}}=P^{-1}.[\Omega]_{|{\vec{e}}}.P
(change of basis formula),
with
P^{-1}=P^{T}
(change of orthonormal basis),
thus
[\Omega]_{|{\vec{b}}}=P^{T}.[\Omega]_{|{\vec{e}}}.P

With

[\Omega]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{b}}_{1}]_{|{\vec{e}}}={1\over\sqrt{b^{2}{+}c^{2}}}\pmatrix{0&-c&b\cr c&0&-a\cr-b&a&0}.\pmatrix{-b\cr a\cr 0}={1\over\sqrt{b^{2}{+}c^{2}}}\pmatrix{-ac\cr-bc\cr a^{2}+b^{2}}=\sqrt{a^{2}{+}b^{2}{+}c^{2}}[{\vec{b}}_{2}]_{|{\vec{e}}}
(expected),

[\Omega]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{b}}_{2}]_{|{\vec{e}}}={1\over\sqrt{b^{2}{+}c^{2}}}{1\over\sqrt{a^{2}{+}b^{2}{+}c^{2}}}\pmatrix{0&-c&b\cr c&0&-a\cr-b&a&0}.\pmatrix{-ac\cr-bc\cr a^{2}+b^{2}}={1\over\sqrt{b^{2}{+}c^{2}}}{1\over\sqrt{a^{2}{+}b^{2}{+}c^{2}}}\pmatrix{bc^{2}+b(a^{2}+b^{2})\cr-ac^{2}-a(a^{2}+b^{2})\cr abc-abc}=-\sqrt{a^{2}{+}b^{2}{+}c^{2}}[{\vec{b}}_{1}]_{|{\vec{e}}}
(expected),
and

[\Omega]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{b}}_{3}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[\vec{0}]
(expected since
{\vec{b}}_{3}\parallel{\vec{\omega}}_{e}
).
Thus
[\Omega]_{|{\vec{e}}}.P=\sqrt{a^{2}{+}b^{2}{+}c^{2}}\pmatrix{[{\vec{b}}_{2}]_{|{\vec{e}}}&-[{\vec{b}}_{1}]_{|{\vec{e}}}&[\vec{0}]_{|{\vec{e}}}}
.
And
(P^{T}.[\Omega]_{|{\vec{e}}}.P)_{ij}=[{\vec{b}}_{i}]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}.[\Omega]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{b}}_{j}]_{|{\vec{e}}}

gives the result.

E.2.5
Curl

Definition E.16

If
{\vec{v}}
is a
C^{1}
vector field,
if
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is a Euclidean basis in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}
,
and if
{\vec{v}}=\sum_{i=1}^{3}v^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,
then the curl (or rotational) of
{\vec{v}}
relative to
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is the vector field

\vec{\rm curl}_{e}{\vec{v}}={\vec{\rm rot}}_{e}{\vec{v}}
given by

\vec{\rm curl}_{e}{\vec{v}}=\sum_{i=1}^{3}({\partial v_{i+2}\over\partial x_{i+1}}-{\partial v_{i+1}\over\partial x_{i+2}})\,{\vec{e}}_{i},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad[\vec{\rm curl}_{e}{\vec{v}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{{\partial v_{3}\over\partial x_{2}}-{\partial v_{2}\over\partial x_{3}}\cr{\partial v_{1}\over\partial x_{3}}-{\partial v_{3}\over\partial x_{1}}\cr{\partial v_{2}\over\partial x_{1}}-{\partial v_{1}\over\partial x_{2}}\cr}.

(E.24)

Proposition E.17

Let
\Omega(t,{p_{t}})={d{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}})-d{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}})^{T}\over 2}
,
and let
{\vec{\omega}}_{e}(t,{p_{t}})
be the associated vector relative to the Euclidean basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
, cf. (
E.21
).
Then

{\vec{\omega}}_{e}={1\over 2}\vec{\rm curl}_{e}{\vec{v}}.

(E.25)

Proof.
(
C.6
) gives

[\Omega]_{|{\vec{e}}}={1\over 2}\pmatrix{0&{\partial v_{1}\over\partial x_{2}}-{\partial v_{2}\over\partial x_{1}}&{\partial v_{1}\over\partial x_{3}}-{\partial v_{3}\over\partial x_{1}}\cr\cdot&0&{\partial v_{2}\over\partial x_{3}}-{\partial v_{3}\over\partial x_{2}}\cr\cdot&\cdot&0\cr}
, with
[\Omega]_{|{\vec{e}}}
antisymmetric.
Thus (
E.22
), (
E.18
) and (
E.24
) gives (
E.25
).

E.3
Pseudo-cross product, and pseudo-vector

Framework:
{\cal M}_{31}
the space of
3*1
matrices, so we leave the vector framework to enter the matrix world.

E.3.1
Definition

Definition E.18

A column matrice is also called a pseudo-vector, or a column vector.

Definition E.19

\displaystyle\pmatrix{x_{1}\cr x_{2}\cr x_{3}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}[{\vec{x}}]

and
\displaystyle\pmatrix{y_{1}\cr y_{2}\cr y_{3}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}[{\vec{y}}]

being two matrices in
{\cal M}_{31}
, their pseudo-cross product is

\pmatrix{x_{1}\cr x_{2}\cr x_{3}}{\displaystyle\mathop{\wedge}^{\circlearrowleft}}\pmatrix{y_{1}\cr y_{2}\cr y_{3}}:=\pmatrix{x_{2}y_{3}-x_{3}y_{2}\cr x_{3}y_{1}-x_{1}y_{3}\cr x_{1}y_{2}-x_{2}y_{1}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}[{\vec{x}}]{\displaystyle\mathop{\wedge}^{\circlearrowleft}}[{\vec{y}}].

(E.26)

Thus the pseudo-cross product of two pseudo-vectors is a pseudo-vector (is a matrix).

E.3.2
Antisymmetric matrix represented by a pseudo-vector

Definition E.20

Let
A=[A_{ij}]=\pmatrix{0&-c&b\cr c&0&-a\cr-b&a&0}
be an antisymmetric matrix (
A_{ji}=-A_{ij}
for all
i,j
).
The pseudo-vecteur
{\displaystyle\mathop{\omega}^{\circlearrowleft}}
associated to
A
is the column matrix

{\displaystyle\mathop{\omega}^{\circlearrowleft}}:=\pmatrix{a\cr b\cr c}.

So,

\framebox{$\displaystyle A.[{\vec{y}}]={\displaystyle\mathop{\omega}^{\circlearrowleft}}\,{\displaystyle\mathop{\wedge}^{\circlearrowleft}}[{\vec{y}}]$},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad A.\pmatrix{y_{1}\cr y_{2}\cr y_{3}}={\displaystyle\mathop{\omega}^{\circlearrowleft}}\,{\displaystyle\mathop{\wedge}^{\circlearrowleft}}\pmatrix{y_{1}\cr y_{2}\cr y_{3}},\quad\hbox{for all matrix }[{\vec{y}}]=\pmatrix{y_{1}\cr y_{2}\cr y_{3}}.

(E.27)

E.3.3
Antisymmetric endomorphism and its pseudo-vectors representations

Let
{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
be our usual affine space,
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
be a Euclidean dot product, and
({\vec{e}}_{i})
be a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Euclidean associated basis.
Let
\Omega
be an antisymmetric endomorphism relative to
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
, so
\Omega^{T}=-\Omega
, cf. (
E.13
).
Thus
[\Omega]_{|{\vec{e}}}
is an antisymmetric matrix. Call
{\displaystyle\mathop{\omega}^{\circlearrowleft}}
the associated pseudo-vector, i.e., cf. (
E.27
), for all
{\vec{y}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}
,

[\Omega]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{y}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}={\displaystyle\mathop{\omega}^{\circlearrowleft}}{\displaystyle\mathop{\wedge}^{\circlearrowleft}}[{\vec{y}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}.

(E.28)

This formula is widely used in mechanics, and unfortunately sometimes noted
\Omega.{\vec{y}}={\vec{\omega}}\wedge{\vec{y}}
(!):

Be careful:
(
E.28
) is
not
a vectorial formula;
This is just a formula for matrix calculations which gives false result if a change of basis is considered;
E.g., with
({\vec{a}}_{1},{\vec{a}}_{2},{\vec{a}}_{3})
be a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Euclidean basis, and

({\vec{b}}_{1},{\vec{b}}_{2},{\vec{b}}_{3})=(-{\vec{a}}_{1},{\vec{a}}_{2},{\vec{a}}_{3})
. So
({\vec{b}}_{i})
is also a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Euclidean basis, but with a different orientation.

1- Vector approach:
Let
P
be the transition matrix from
({\vec{a}}_{i})
to
({\vec{b}}_{i})
,
so
P=\pmatrix{-1&0&0\cr 0&1&0\cr 0&0&1}
.
Let
[\Omega]_{|{\vec{a}}}=\pmatrix{0&-c&b\cr c&0&-a\cr-b&a&0}
.
Thus,
\Omega
being an endomorphism, the change of basis formula gives

[\Omega]_{|{\vec{b}}}=P^{-1}.[\Omega]_{|{\vec{a}}}.P=\pmatrix{-1&0&0\cr 0&1&0\cr 0&0&1}.\pmatrix{0&-c&b\cr c&0&-a\cr-b&a&0}.\pmatrix{-1&0&0\cr 0&1&0\cr 0&0&1}=\pmatrix{0&c&-b\cr-c&0&-a\cr b&a&0}.

(E.29)

Thus the vectors
{\vec{\omega}}_{a}
and
{\vec{\omega}}_{b}
are given by (
E.22
):

[{\vec{\omega}}_{a}]_{|{\vec{a}}}=\pmatrix{a\cr b\cr c},\quad[{\vec{\omega}}_{b}]_{|{\vec{b}}}=\pmatrix{a\cr-b\cr-c},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&{\vec{\omega}}_{a}=a{\vec{a}}_{1}+b{\vec{a}}_{2}+c{\vec{a}}_{3},\cr&{\vec{\omega}}_{b}=a{\vec{b}}_{1}-b{\vec{b}}_{2}-c{\vec{b}}_{3},\crcr}}\,\right\}\quad\hbox{thus}\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle{\vec{\omega}}_{b}=-{\vec{\omega}}_{a}$}.

(E.30)

2- Matrix approach (
E.27
) gives

[\Omega]_{|{\vec{a}}}.[{\vec{y}}]={\displaystyle\mathop{\omega}^{\circlearrowleft}}_{a}\,{\displaystyle\mathop{\wedge}^{\circlearrowleft}}[{\vec{y}}]
and

[\Omega]_{|{\vec{b}}}.[{\vec{y}}]={\displaystyle\mathop{\omega}^{\circlearrowleft}}_{b}\,{\displaystyle\mathop{\wedge}^{\circlearrowleft}}[{\vec{y}}]
,
with

{\displaystyle\mathop{\omega}^{\circlearrowleft}}_{a}=\pmatrix{a\cr b\cr c}\quad\hbox{and}\quad{\displaystyle\mathop{\omega}^{\circlearrowleft}}_{b}=\pmatrix{a\cr-b\cr-c},\quad\hbox{so}\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathop{\omega}^{\circlearrowleft}}_{a}\neq-{\displaystyle\mathop{\omega}^{\circlearrowleft}}_{b}$}.

(E.31)

And
{\displaystyle\mathop{\omega}^{\circlearrowleft}}
does not represent a single vector either, since it does not satisfy the vector change
of basis formula
{\displaystyle\mathop{\omega}^{\circlearrowleft}}_{b}\neq P^{-1}.{\displaystyle\mathop{\omega}^{\circlearrowleft}}_{a}
. Thus
{\displaystyle\mathop{\omega}^{\circlearrowleft}}
is not a vector (is not tensorial): It is just a matrix
(called a “pseudo-vector”).

E.4
Examples

E.4.1
Rectilinear motion

Let
{\widetilde{\Phi}}:[t_{1},t_{2}]\times{\it O\!b\!j}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
be a
C^{1}
motion.
Let
{t_{0}}\in]t_{1},t_{2}[
and
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}\in{\it O\!b\!j}
.

Definition E.21

The motion of
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
is rectilinear iff, for all
{t_{0}},t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]
,

{{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}(t)-{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}({t_{0}})\over t{-}{t_{0}}}\parallel{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}{}^{\prime}({t_{0}}).

(E.32)

And the motion is rectilinear uniform iff, for all
{t_{0}},t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]
,

{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}(t)={\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}({t_{0}})+(t{-}{t_{0}})\,{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\!{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}}{}^{\prime}({t_{0}}),\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad p(t)=p({t_{0}})+(t{-}{t_{0}})\,{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}}({t_{0}},p({t_{0}}))

(E.33)

when
p(t)={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
,
that is, the trajectory is traveled at constant velocity.

E.4.2
Circular motion

Let
({\vec{E}}_{1},{\vec{E}}_{2})
be a Euclidean basis.
Let
{t_{0}}\in[t_{1},t_{2}]
.
A motion
{\Phi^{t_{0}}}
is a circular motion iff

\overrightarrow{{\cal O}{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{P}}(t)}=x(t){\vec{E}}_{1}+y(t){\vec{E}}_{2},\quad[\overrightarrow{{\cal O}{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{P}}(t)}]_{|{\vec{E}}}=\pmatrix{x(t)=a+R\cos(\theta(t))\cr y(t)=b+R\sin(\theta(t))},

(E.34)

for some
R>0
(called the radius), some
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
,
and some function
\theta:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.
And
\pmatrix{a\cr b}={\cal O}_{C}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
is the center of the circle
and
\theta(t)
is the angle at
t
.
And the particle
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
(s.t.
{\widetilde{\Phi}}({t_{0}},{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})=P
) stays on the circle with center
{\cal O}_{C}
and radius
R
.

The circular motion is uniforme iff, for all
t
,
\theta^{\prime\prime}(t)=0
,
that is,
\exists\omega_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\forall t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]
,
\theta(t)=\omega_{0}t
.

Notation:

{\vec{\varphi}}^{t_{0}}_{P}(t)=R\cos(\theta(t){\vec{E}}_{1}+R\sin(\theta(t)){\vec{E}}_{2},\quad\hbox{so}\quad[{\vec{\varphi}}^{t_{0}}_{P}(t)]_{|{\vec{E}}}=\pmatrix{R\cos(\theta(t))\cr R\sin(\theta(t))}.

(E.35)

Thus the Lagrangian velocity of a circular motion is

{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}}_{P}(t)=({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{\prime}(t)=({\vec{\varphi}}^{t_{0}}_{P})^{\prime}(t),\quad\hbox{so}\quad[{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}}_{P}(t)]_{|{\vec{E}}}=R\theta^{\prime}(t)\pmatrix{-\sin(\theta(t))\cr\cos(\theta(t))},

(E.36)

and
{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}}_{P}(t)
is orthogonal to
{\vec{\varphi}}^{t_{0}}_{P}(t)
(the radius vector).
And the Lagrangian acceleration is

{\vec{\Gamma}^{\,t_{0}}}_{P}(t)=R\theta^{\prime\prime}(t)\pmatrix{-\sin(\theta(t))\cr\cos(\theta(t))}+R(\theta^{\prime}(t))^{2}\pmatrix{-\cos(\theta(t))\cr-\sin(\theta(t))}.

(E.37)

Consider

{\vec{e}}_{r}(t)={{\vec{\varphi}}^{t_{0}}_{P}(t)\over||{\vec{\varphi}}^{t_{0}}_{P}(t)||}=\pmatrix{\cos(\theta(t))\cr\sin(\theta(t))},\quad\hbox{and}\quad{\vec{e}}_{\theta}(t)=\pmatrix{-\sin(\theta(t))\cr\cos(\theta(t))},

(E.38)

thus
({\vec{e}}_{r}(t),{\vec{e}}_{\theta}(t))
is an orthonormal basis.
Then :

{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}_{P}(t)=R\theta^{\prime}(t)\,{\vec{e}}_{\theta}(t),

(E.39)

and :

{\vec{\Gamma}^{\,t_{0}}}_{P}(t)=-R(\theta^{\prime}(t))^{2}\,{\vec{e}}_{r}(t)+R\theta^{\prime\prime}(t)\,{\vec{e}}_{\theta}(t).

(E.40)

E.g., in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and a motion in he “horizontal” plane given by
({\vec{e}}_{1},{\vec{e}}_{2})
, the vertical line being given by
{\vec{E}}_{3}
.
Here

{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}_{P}(t)={\vec{\omega}}(t)\wedge{\vec{\varphi}}^{t_{0}}_{P}(t),\quad\hbox{where}\quad{\vec{\omega}}(t)=\omega(t){\vec{e}}_{3}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\omega(t)=\theta^{\prime}(t).

(E.41)

And

{\vec{\Gamma}^{\,t_{0}}}(t)={d{\vec{\omega}}\over dt}(t)\wedge{\vec{\varphi}}^{t_{0}}_{P}(t)+{\vec{\omega}}(t)\wedge{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}_{P}(t)\qquad(=R{d\omega\over dt}(t)\,{\vec{e}}_{\theta}(t)-\omega^{2}(t)R\,{\vec{e}}_{r}(t)).

(E.42)

E.4.3
Motion of a planet (centripetal acceleration)

Illustration:
{\it O\!b\!j}
is e.g. a planet from the solar system.

Let
({\vec{e}}_{1},{\vec{e}}_{2},{\vec{e}}_{3})
be a Euclidean basis (e.g. fixed relative to stars an
({\vec{e}}_{1},{\vec{e}}_{2})

define the ecliptic plane),
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
be the Euclidean associated dot product,

||.||
the Euclidean associated norm,
{\cal O}
an origin in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
(e.g. the center of the Sun),
and
{\cal R}=({\cal O},({\vec{e}}_{i}))
.

Consider a motion
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
of
{\it O\!b\!j}
in
{\cal R}
, cf. (
1.5
).
Let
{t_{0}}\in[t_{1},t_{1}]
, and consider
{\Phi^{t_{0}}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\Phi
or
{{\vec{\varphi}}^{\,t_{0}}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{\varphi}}
, cf. (
3.1
)-(
3.4
).

Definition E.22

The motion of a particle
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
is a centripetal acceleration motion iff
the particle is not static and, at all time, its acceleration vector
{\vec{A}}(t)

points to a fixed point
F
(focus).

We will take the focus
F
as the origin of the referential, that is,
{\cal O}:=F
.

Thus, for all
t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]
,
\overrightarrow{{\cal O}\Phi_{P}(t)}\parallel{\vec{A}}_{P}(t)
, that is,

\overrightarrow{{\cal O}\Phi_{P}(t)}\wedge{\vec{A}}_{P}(t)=\vec{0}.

(E.43)

Remark E.23

A rectilinear motion is a centripetal acceleration motion,
but such a motion is usually excluded in the definition
E.22
.

Example E.24

The motion of a planet from the solar system is a centripetal acceleration motion:
An elliptical motion of focus the center of the Sun.

Example E.25

The second Newton’s law of motion
\sum{\vec{f}}=m{\vec{\gamma}}
(Galilean referential) gives:
If
\sum{\vec{f}}
is, at all time, directed to a unique point
F
,
then the motion is a centripetal acceleration motion.

Let
\Phi
be a centripetal acceleration motion,
let
{\cal O}
be the focus, and let
{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t):=\overrightarrow{{\cal O}\Phi_{P}(t)}
.
So the Lagrangian velocity and acceleration are

{\vec{V}}_{P}(t)={d\Phi_{P}\over dt}(t)={d{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}\over dt}(t),\quad\hbox{and}\quad{\vec{A}}_{P}(t)={d^{2}\Phi_{P}\over dt^{2}}(t)={d^{2}{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}\over dt^{2}}(t),

(E.44)

and
{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t)\wedge{\vec{A}}_{P}(t)=\vec{0}
, cf. (
E.43
).

Definition E.26

The areolar velocity at
t
is the vector

{\vec{Z}}(t)={1\over 2}{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t)\wedge{\vec{V}}_{P}(t).

(E.45)

Proposition E.27

If
\Phi
is a centripetal acceleration motion, then the areolar velocity is contant,
that is,
{d{\vec{Z}}\over dt}(t)=\vec{0}
pour tout
t
, so

{\vec{Z}}(t)={\vec{Z}}({t_{0}}),\quad\forall t.

(E.46)

That is, the position vectors sweep equal areas in equal times.
And
{\vec{Z}}(t_{0})=\vec{0}
iff
\Phi
is a rectilinear motion.

If
{\vec{Z}}(t_{0})\neq\vec{0}
then :

-
{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t)
and
{\vec{V}}_{P}(t)
are orthogonal to
{\vec{Z}}(t_{0})
at all time
t
,

- The motion of the particle
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
takes place in the affine plane orthogonal to
{\vec{Z}}(t_{0})
passing through
O
.

-
{\vec{V}}_{P}(t)
never vanishes.

Proof.
(
E.45
) and (
E.43
) give

2{d{\vec{Z}}\over dt}(t)={d{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}\over dt}(t)\wedge{\vec{V}}_{P}(t)+{\vec{\varphi}}(t)\wedge{d{\vec{V}}_{P}\over dt}(t)={\vec{V}}_{P}(t)\wedge{\vec{V}}_{P}(t)+{\vec{\varphi}}(t)\wedge{\vec{A}}_{P}(t)=\vec{0}+\vec{0}
. Thus
{\vec{Z}}
is constant,
{\vec{Z}}(t)={\vec{Z}}(t_{0})
for all
t
.
Then, if
{\vec{Z}}({t_{0}})\neq\vec{0}
then
{\vec{Z}}(t)\neq\vec{0}
pour tout
t
, and

\bullet

{\vec{Z}}(t)={1\over 2}{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t)\wedge{\vec{V}}_{P}(t)
gives that
{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t)
et
{\vec{V}}_{P}(t)
are orthogonal to
{\vec{Z}}(t_{0})
for all
t
, thus
{\vec{A}}_{P}(t)
is orthogonal to
{\vec{Z}}(t_{0})
, cf. (
E.43
).

\bullet
The Taylor expansion reads

{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t)={\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t_{0})+{\vec{V}}_{P}(t_{0})(t{-}t_{0})+\int_{\tau=t_{0}}^{t}{\vec{A}}_{P}(\tau)(t{-}\tau)^{2}\,d\tau
,
with
{\vec{V}}_{P}(t_{0})
and
{\vec{A}}_{P}(\tau)

\perp{\vec{Z}}(t_{0})
for all
\tau
,
thus
{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t)-{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t_{0})\perp{\vec{Z}}(t_{0})
for all
\tau
,
that is
\overrightarrow{{\cal O}p(t)}-\overrightarrow{{\cal O}P}=\overrightarrow{Pp(t)}\perp{\vec{Z}}(t_{0})
for all
\tau
,
Thus
p(t)
belongs to the affine plane containing
P
orthogonal to
{\vec{Z}}(t_{0})
, for all
t
.
And
\overrightarrow{{\cal O}P}={\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t_{0})\perp{\vec{Z}}(t_{0})
, thus
O
belong to the same plane.

\bullet

{\vec{Z}}(t)={\vec{Z}}(t_{0})\neq\vec{0}
implies
{\vec{V}}_{P}(t)\neq\vec{0}
for all
t
,
and (
E.45
) gives:
({\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t),{\vec{V}}_{P}(t),{\vec{Z}}(t_{0}))
is a positively-oriented basis.
Since
{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}
and
{\vec{V}}
are continuous and do not vanish, since
{\vec{Z}}(t_{0})\neq\vec{0}
,
we get:
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}
“turns around
{\vec{Z}}(t_{0})
” and keeps its direction.

If
{\vec{Z}}(t)=\vec{0}
then
{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t)\parallel{\vec{V}}_{P}(t)
for all
t
, cf. (
E.45
),
so
{\vec{V}}_{P}(t)=f(t){\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t)
where
f
is some scalar function.
And
{\vec{V}}_{P}(t)={\vec{\varphi}}_{P}{}^{\prime}(t)
gives
{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}{}^{\prime}(t)=f(t){\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t)
,
thus
{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t)={\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t_{0})e^{F(t)}
where
F
is a primitive of
f
s.t.
F({t_{0}})=0
, thus

{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t)\parallel{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t_{0})
, so

\overrightarrow{{\cal O}\Phi_{P}(t)}\parallel\overrightarrow{{\cal O}\Phi_{P}({t_{0}})}
, for all
t
: The motion is rectilinear.

Interpretation.
(Non rectilinear motion.)

The area swept by
{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t)
is, at first order,
the area of the triangle whose sides are

{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t)
and
{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t+\tau)
(“anglular sector”).
So, with
\tau
close to
0
, let

{\vec{S}}_{t}(\tau)={1\over 2}{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t)\wedge{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t+\tau),\quad\hbox{and}\quad S_{t}(\tau)=||{\vec{S}}_{t}(\tau)||,

(E.47)

the vectorial an scalar area.
With
{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t{+}\tau)={\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t)+{\vec{V}}_{P}(t)\tau+o(\tau)
we get

{\vec{S}}_{t}(\tau)={1\over 2}{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t)\wedge({\vec{V}}_{P}(t)\tau+o(\tau)),

(E.48)

Since
{\vec{S}}_{t}(0)=0
we get

{{\vec{S}}_{t}(\tau)-{\vec{S}}(0)\over\tau}={1\over 2}{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t)\wedge{\vec{V}}_{P}(t)+o(1)
, then

{d{\vec{S}}_{t}\over d\tau}(0)={1\over 2}{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t)\wedge{\vec{V}}_{P}(t)={\vec{Z}}(t)={\vec{Z}}(t_{0}),

(E.49)

thanks to (
E.46
), thus

{d{\vec{S}}_{t}\over d\tau}(0)={d{\vec{S}}_{t_{0}}\over d\tau}(0),\qquad\forall t\in[t_{0},T],

(E.50)

that is, the rate of variation of
{\vec{S}}_{t}
is constant.
And with
||{\vec{S}}_{t}(\Delta\tau)||^{2}=({\vec{S}}_{t}(\Delta\tau),{\vec{S}}_{t}(\Delta\tau))
we get

{d||{\vec{S}}_{t}||^{2}\over d\tau}(\Delta\tau)=2({d{\vec{S}}_{t}\over d\tau}(\Delta\tau),{\vec{S}}_{t}(\Delta\tau)),

(E.51)

so, since
{\vec{S}}_{t}(0)=0
,

{d||{\vec{S}}_{t}||^{2}\over d\tau}(0)=0.

(E.52)

Therefore the function
t\rightarrow||{\vec{S}}_{t}(0)||^{2}=S_{t}(0)^{2}
is constant,
thus
t\rightarrow S_{t}(0)
est constant, and

{dS_{t}\over d\tau}(0)
is constant.

Exercice E.28

Give a parametrization of the swept area, and redo the calculations.

Answer
. Let

r(t)=||{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t)||,\hskip 9.24994pt\theta(t)=\widehat{p(t)OP}\hskip 9.24994pt\hbox{(angle)},

(E.53)

then

{\vec{\varphi}}_{P}(t)=\pmatrix{r(t)\cos(\theta(t))\cr r(t)\sin(\theta(t))\cr 0}.

(E.54)

Thus

{\vec{V}}_{P}(t)=\pmatrix{r^{\prime}(t)\cos(\theta(t)-r(t))\theta^{\prime}(t)\sin(\theta(t))\cr r^{\prime}(t)\sin(\theta(t)+r(t))\theta^{\prime}(t)\cos(\theta(t))\cr 0}.

(E.55)

With (
E.45
) we get

{\vec{Z}}(t)={1\over 2}\pmatrix{0\cr 0\cr r^{2}(t)\theta^{\prime}(t)},\hskip 9.24994pt\hbox{with}\hskip 9.24994ptr^{2}(t)\theta^{\prime}(t)=r^{2}(t_{0})\theta^{\prime}(t_{0})\hskip 9.24994pt\hbox{(constant)},

(E.56)

cf. (
E.46
).
A parametrization of the swept area is then

{\vec{\cal A}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr[0,1]\times[t_{0},T]&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}\cr(\rho,t)&\rightarrow{\vec{\cal A}}(\rho,t)\cr}}\,\right\},\hskip 18.49988pt{\vec{\cal A}}(\rho,t)=\pmatrix{\rho\,r(t)\cos(\theta(t))\cr\rho\,r(t)\sin(\theta(t))\cr 0}.

(E.57)

Therefore, the tangent associated vectors are

{\partial{\vec{\cal A}}\over\partial\rho}(\rho,t)=\pmatrix{r(t)\cos(\theta(t))\cr r(t)\sin(\theta(t))\cr 0},\hskip 18.49988pt{\partial{\vec{\cal A}}\over\partial t}(\rho,t)=\pmatrix{\rho r^{\prime}(t)\cos(\theta(t)-\rho r(t))\theta^{\prime}(t)\sin(\theta(t))\cr\rho r^{\prime}(t)\sin(\theta(t)+\rho r(t))\theta^{\prime}(t)\cos(\theta(t))\cr 0},

(E.58)

hence the vectorial and scalare element areas are

d\vec{\sigma}=({\partial{\vec{\cal A}}\over\partial\rho}\wedge{\partial{\vec{\cal A}}\over\partial t})d\rho dt=\pmatrix{0\cr 0\cr\rho r^{2}\theta^{\prime}\,d\rho dt},\hskip 18.49988ptd\sigma=\rho r^{2}\theta^{\prime}\,d\rho d\theta.

(E.59)

Therefore the area between
t_{0}
and
t
is

{\cal A}(t)={\cal A}(t_{0})+\int_{\rho=0}^{1}\int_{\tau=t_{0}}^{t}\rho r^{2}(\tau)\theta^{\prime}(\tau)\,d\rho d\tau={1\over 2}\int_{\tau=t_{0}}^{t}r(\tau)^{2}\theta^{\prime}(\tau)\,d\tau.

(E.60)

Hence

{\cal A}^{\prime}(t)=r(t)^{2}\theta^{\prime}(t)=r(t_{0})^{2}\theta^{\prime}(t_{0})\hskip 9.24994pt(=\hbox{constant}=||{\vec{Z}}({t_{0}})||),

(E.61)

cf. (
E.56
).

Exercice E.29

Prove the Binet formulas (non rectilinear central motion):

V_{P}(t)^{2}=Z_{0}^{2}\Bigl({1\over r^{2}}+({d{1\over r}\over d\theta}\,)^{2}\Bigr)(t),\qquad{\vec{\Gamma}}_{P}(t)=-{Z_{0}^{2}\over r^{2}}\Bigl({1\over r}+{d^{2}{1\over r}\over d\theta^{2}}\Bigr)(t)\,{\vec{e}}_{r}(t),

(E.62)

for the energy and the acceleration.

Answer
. Proposition
E.27
tells that
\Phi
is a planar motion.
With (
E.53
) and
{\vec{e}}_{r}(t)=\pmatrix{\cos(\theta(t))\cr\sin(\theta(t))}

we have
{\vec{\varphi}}(t)=r(t){\vec{e}}_{r}(t)
(in the plane).
Let
{\vec{e}}_{\theta}(t)=\pmatrix{-\sin(\theta(t))\cr\cos(\theta(t))}
, thus

{\vec{V}}(t)={dr\over dt}(t){\vec{e}}_{r}(t)+r(t){d{\vec{e}}_{r}\over dt}(t)=r^{\prime}(t){\vec{e}}_{r}(t)+r(t)\theta^{\prime}(t){\vec{e}}_{\theta}(t).

And
{\vec{e}}_{r}(t)\perp{\vec{e}}_{\theta}(t)
gives

V^{2}(t)=(r^{\prime}(t))^{2}+(r(t)\theta^{\prime}(t))^{2}.

Since
\theta^{\prime}(t)\neq 0
for all
t
(non rectilinear central motion)
Let
s(\theta(t))=r(t)
.
Let us suppose that
\theta
is
C^{1}
, thus
\theta^{\prime}>0
or
\theta^{\prime}<0
,
and
\theta:t\rightarrow\theta(t)
defines a change of variable. And

r^{\prime}(t)=s^{\prime}(\theta(t))\theta^{\prime}(t).

And (
E.61
) and
\theta^{\prime}(t)={Z_{0}\over r^{2}(t)}
give

V^{2}(t(\theta))=(s^{\prime}(\theta))^{2}{Z_{0}^{2}\over r^{4}(t)}+r^{2}(t){Z_{0}^{2}\over r^{4}(t)}=Z_{0}^{2}({(s^{\prime}(\theta))^{2}\over s^{4}(\theta)}+{1\over s^{2}(\theta)})=Z_{0}^{2}[\Bigl({d{1\over s}\over d\theta}(\theta)\Bigr)^{2}+{1\over s^{2}(\theta)}].

Thus
r(t)=s(\theta)
and
{dr\over d\theta}:={ds\over d\theta}

give the first Binet formula. Then

{\vec{\Gamma}}(t)=r^{\prime\prime}(t){\vec{e}}_{r}(t)+r^{\prime}(t){d{\vec{e}}_{r}\over dt}(t)+(r^{\prime}(t)\theta^{\prime}(t)+r(t)\theta^{\prime\prime}(t)){\vec{e}}_{\theta}(t)+r(t)\theta^{\prime}(t){d{\vec{e}}_{\theta}\over dt}(t),

with
\displaystyle{d{\vec{e}}_{r}\over dt}\parallel{\vec{e}}_{\theta}
,
and
\displaystyle{d{\vec{e}}_{\theta}\over dt}(t)=-\theta^{\prime}(t){\vec{e}}_{r}(t)
,
and
{\vec{e}}_{\theta}\perp{\vec{\Gamma}}
(central motion), we get

{\vec{\Gamma}}(t)=(r^{\prime\prime}(t)-r(t)(\theta^{\prime}(t))^{2}){\vec{e}}_{r}(t).

And

r^{\prime}(t)=s^{\prime}(\theta)\theta^{\prime}(t)=s^{\prime}(\theta){Z_{0}\over r^{2}(t)}=Z_{0}{s^{\prime}(\theta)\over s^{2}(\theta)}=-Z_{0}{d{1\over s}\over d\theta}(\theta),

thus

r^{\prime\prime}(t)=-Z_{0}{d^{2}{1\over s}\over d\theta^{2}}(\theta)\,\theta^{\prime}(t)=-{Z_{0}^{2}\over r^{2}(t)}{d^{2}{1\over s}\over d\theta^{2}}(\theta),

which is the second Binet formula.

Appendix F
Riesz representation theorem

F.1
The Riesz representation theorem

Framework:
(E,(\cdot,\cdot)_{g})
is Hilbert space, i.e.
E
is a vector space equipped with an inner dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
such that,
with the associated norm defined by
||{\vec{v}}||_{g}:=\sqrt{({\vec{v}},{\vec{v}})_{g}}
,
(E,||.||_{g})
is a complete space. And
{E^{*}}={\cal L}(E;\mathbb{R})
is the space of the linear and continuous forms on
E
(the space of linear “measuring tools”) equipped with its norm
\displaystyle||\ell||_{E^{*}}:=\sup_{||{\vec{x}}||_{g}=1}|\ell.{\vec{x}}|<\infty
.

\bullet
We have the easy statement:

\forall{\vec{v}}\in E\hbox{ (vector),}\;\;\exists!v_{g}\in E^{*}\hbox{ (linear continuous form)}\quad\hbox{s{.}t{.}}\quad v_{g}.{\vec{x}}=({\vec{v}},{\vec{x}})_{g},\;\;\forall{\vec{x}}\in E,

(F.1)

and
||v_{g}||_{E^{*}}=||{\vec{v}}||_{g}
.
(Usual notation in finite dimension:
v_{g}.{\vec{x}}={\vec{v}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,{\vec{x}}
, or simply
v.{\vec{x}}={\vec{v}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{\vec{x}}
if a chosen
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
is imposed to all observers.)

Indeed: Define
v_{g}:E\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by
v_{g}({\vec{x}})=({\vec{v}},{\vec{x}})_{g}
for all
{\vec{x}}\in E
; The definition domain of
v_{g}
is
E
and
v_{g}
is trivially linear; And the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality gives

|v_{g}({\vec{x}})|=|({\vec{v}},{\vec{x}})_{g}|\leq||{\vec{v}}||_{g}\,||{\vec{x}}||_{g}
for all
{\vec{x}}\in E
, thus
||v_{g}||_{E^{*}}\leq||{\vec{v}}||_{g}<\infty
, thus
v_{g}
is continuous;
And
|v_{g}({\vec{v}})|=|({\vec{v}},{\vec{v}})_{g}|=||{\vec{v}}||_{g}\,||{\vec{v}}||_{g}
, thus
||v_{g}||_{E^{*}}\geq||{\vec{v}}||_{g}
, thus
||v_{g}||_{E^{*}}=||{\vec{v}}||_{g}
.

\bullet
The Riesz representation theorem concerns the converse: If you choose an inner dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
in
E
(e.g. English of French), then you can represent a “measuring instrument”
\ell\in E^{*}
by a vector
{\vec{\ell}}_{g}\in E
:

Theorem F.1
(Riesz representation theorem, and definition)

(E,(\cdot,\cdot)_{g})
being a Hilbert space,

\forall\ell\in{E^{*}}\hbox{ (linear continuous form),}\;\;\exists!{\vec{\ell}}_{g}\in E\hbox{ (vector)}\quad\hbox{s{.}t{.}}\quad\ell.{\vec{x}}=({\vec{\ell}}_{g},{\vec{x}})_{g},\;\;\forall{\vec{x}}\in E,

(F.2)

and
||{\vec{\ell}}_{g}||_{g}=||\ell||_{E^{*}}
.
And
{\vec{\ell}}_{g}
is called the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Riesz representation vector of
\ell
(depends on
g
).

(Usual notation in finite dimension:
v_{g}.{\vec{x}}={\vec{v}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,{\vec{x}}
, or simply
v.{\vec{x}}={\vec{v}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{\vec{x}}
if a chosen
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
is imposed to all observers.)

Proof.
Easy in finite dimension: With a basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
, if
[\ell]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{\ell_{1}&\dots&\ell_{n}}
(row matrix since
\ell
is a linear form) then (
F.2
) gives
[\ell]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[{\vec{\ell}}_{g}]_{{\vec{e}}}^{T}.[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}
, thus
[{\vec{\ell}}_{g}]_{{\vec{e}}}=[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{-1}.[\ell]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}
(column matrix), thus
{\vec{\ell}}_{g}
.

General case (e.g. with
E={L^{2}(\Omega)}
and the finite element method):
If
\ell=0
then
{\vec{\ell}}_{g}=\vec{0}
(trivial).
Suppose
\ell\neq 0
: Thus
{\rm Ker}\ell=\ell^{-1}(\{0\})\neq\{\vec{0}\}
(the kernel).
If
\dim E=1
, it is trivial (exercise). Suppose
\dim E\geq 2
.
Since
\ell
is continuous, its kernel
{\rm Ker}\ell=\ell^{-1}(\{0\})
is closed
in
E
.
Thus, if
{\vec{x}}\in E
, then its
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-orthogonal projection
{\vec{x}}_{0}\in{\rm Ker}\ell
on
{\rm Ker}\ell
exists, is unique, and is given by:

\forall{\vec{y}}_{0}\in{\rm Ker}\ell,\;({\vec{x}}-{\vec{x}}_{0},{\vec{y}}_{0})_{g}=0.

(So
{\vec{x}}-{\vec{x}}_{0}\perp_{g}{\rm Ker}\ell
.)
Choose a
{\vec{x}}\notin{\rm Ker}\ell
(possible since
\ell\neq 0
), and let
{\vec{n}}:={{\vec{x}}-{\vec{x}}_{0}\over||{\vec{x}}-{\vec{x}}_{0}||_{g}}
;
So
{\vec{n}}
is a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-orthonormal vector to
{\rm Ker}\ell
, and
({\rm Ker}\ell)^{\perp}={\rm Vect}\{{\vec{n}}\}

since
\dim({\rm Ker}\ell)^{\perp}=1

(in finite dimension cf. the Dimension Formula which states that the dimension of the domain of a linear map is the sum of the dimension of its range and the dimension of its kernel, and in infinite dimension see next exercise
F.2
).
And
E={\rm Ker}\ell\oplus({\rm Ker}\ell)^{\perp}
since both vector spaces are closed (an orthogonal is always closed in a Hilbert space).
Thus if
{\vec{x}}\in E
then
{\vec{x}}={\vec{x}}_{0}+\lambda{\vec{n}}\in{\rm Ker}\ell\oplus({\rm Ker}\ell)^{\perp}
;
Thus
({\vec{x}},{\vec{n}})_{g}=\lambda
and
\ell({\vec{x}})=0+\lambda\ell({\vec{n}})=({\vec{x}},{\vec{n}})_{g}\ell({\vec{n}})=({\vec{x}},\ell({\vec{n}}){\vec{n}})_{g}
(bilinearity of
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
);
Thus
{\vec{\ell}}_{g}:=\ell({\vec{n}}){\vec{n}}
satisfies (
F.2
).
And if
{\vec{\ell}}_{g1}
and
{\vec{\ell}}_{g2}
satisfy (
F.2
)
then
({\vec{\ell}}_{g1}-{\vec{\ell}}_{g2},{\vec{x}})_{g}=0
for all
{\vec{x}}\in E
, thus
{\vec{\ell}}_{g1}-{\vec{\ell}}_{g2}=0
.
Thus
{\vec{\ell}}_{g}
is unique.

And the Cauchy–Schwarz theorem give
||\ell||_{E^{*}}:=\sup_{||{\vec{x}}||_{g}=1}|\ell({\vec{x}})|=\sup_{||{\vec{x}}||_{g}=1}|({\vec{\ell}}_{g},{\vec{x}})_{g}|=||{\vec{\ell}}_{g}||_{g}
.

{\vec{R}}_{g}
is an isomorphism between Banach spaces:
linearity since

({\vec{R}}_{g}(\ell+\lambda m),{\vec{x}})_{g}=(\ell+\lambda m).{\vec{x}}=\ell.{\vec{x}}+\lambda m.{\vec{x}}=({\vec{R}}_{g}(\ell),{\vec{x}})_{g}+\lambda({\vec{R}}_{g}(m),{\vec{x}})_{g}=({\vec{R}}_{g}(\ell)+\lambda{\vec{R}}_{g}(m),{\vec{x}})_{g}
for all
{\vec{x}}
gives
{\vec{R}}_{g}(\ell+\lambda m)={\vec{R}}_{g}(\ell)+\lambda{\vec{R}}_{g}(m)
,
bijectivity thanks to (
F.1
) and (
F.2
), and the norm is kept since
||{\vec{\ell}}_{g}||_{g}=||\ell||_{E^{*}}
.

Exercice F.2

Prove: If
\ell\in E^{*}{-}\{0\}
then

\dim({\rm Ker}\ell)^{\perp}=1
(
=\dim({\rm Im}(\ell))=\dim\mathbb{R}
).

Answer
. Consider the restriction

\ell_{|{\rm Ker}\ell^{\perp}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr({\rm Ker}\ell)^{\perp}&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr{\vec{x}}&\rightarrow\ell_{|{\rm Ker}\ell^{\perp}}.{\vec{x}}=\ell.{\vec{x}}\crcr}}\,\right\}
.
It is linear (since
\ell
is), and thus one to one: Indeed it is onto since
\ell\neq 0
, and it is one to one since
if
\ell_{|{\rm Ker}\ell^{\perp}}({\vec{x}})=0=\ell({\vec{x}})
then
{\vec{x}}\in({\rm Ker}\ell)^{\perp}\bigcap{\rm Ker}\ell=\{\vec{0}\}
, thus
{\vec{x}}=0
.
Thus
\dim({\rm Ker}\ell)^{\perp}\leq\dim({\rm Im}(\ell))=1
: Indeed, if
{\vec{z}}_{1},{\vec{z}}_{2}\in({\rm Ker}\ell)^{\perp}{-}\{\vec{0}\}
then
\ell_{|{\rm Ker}\ell^{\perp}}({\vec{z}}_{1})\in\mathbb{R}
and
\ell_{|{\rm Ker}\ell^{\perp}}({\vec{z}}_{2})\in\mathbb{R}
, thus

\exists\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
s.t.
\ell_{|{\rm Ker}\ell^{\perp}}({\vec{z}}_{2})=\lambda\ell_{|{\rm Ker}\ell^{\perp}}({\vec{z}}_{1})
,
thus
\ell_{|{\rm Ker}\ell^{\perp}}({\vec{z}}_{2}-\lambda{\vec{z}}_{1})=0
, thus
{\vec{z}}_{2}-\lambda{\vec{z}}_{1}=\vec{0}

since
\ell_{|{\rm Ker}\ell^{\perp}}
is one to one.
And
{\vec{n}}\in({\rm Ker}\ell)^{\perp}
gives
\dim({\rm Ker}\ell)^{\perp}\geq 1
(above proof). Thus
\dim({\rm Ker}\ell)^{\perp}=1={\rm Vect}\{{\vec{n}}\}
.

F.2
The Riesz representation operator

The Riesz representation theorem
F.1
gives the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Riesz representation operator

{\vec{R}}_{g}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{E^{*}}&\rightarrow E\cr\ell&\rightarrow{\vec{R}}_{g}(\ell):={\vec{\ell}}_{g},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad({\vec{R}}_{g}(\ell),{\vec{v}})_{g}=\ell.{\vec{v}},\;\;\forall{\vec{v}}\in E.\crcr}}\,\right.

(F.3)

So
{\vec{R}}_{g}
transforms a << covariant
\ell
>> into a << contravariant
{\vec{\ell}}_{g}
>> thanks to the tool
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
.

NB (fundamental):
{\vec{R}}_{g}
is a isomorphism between the Banach spaces
(E,||.||_{g})
and
(E^{*},||.||_{E^{*}})
,
but
{\vec{R}}_{g}
is
not
canonical since it requires a man made tool (an inner dot product chosen by some observer) to be defined.
(An isomorphism
E\leftrightarrow E^{*}
can
never
be canonical, see §
T.2
.)

And with
{\cal G}
the set of inner dot products in
E
, we have thus defined the Riesz representation mapping

{\vec{R}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\cal G}\times{E^{*}}&\rightarrow E\cr(g,\ell)&\rightarrow{\vec{R}}(g,\ell):={\vec{\ell}}_{g}={\vec{R}}_{g}(\ell)={\vec{\ell}}(g).\cr}}\,\right.

(F.4)

So
{\vec{R}}
has two inputs: A choice
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
by an observer for the first slot, a linear form for the second slot.

F.3
Quantification with a basis

Here
E
is finite dimensional,
\dim E=n
,
\ell\in{E^{*}}
(a linear form),
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
is an inner dot product,

({\vec{e}}_{i})
is a basis,
(\pi_{ei})
is the dual basis (classical notations). Let

g_{ij}=g({\vec{e}}_{i},{\vec{e}}_{j}),\quad\ell={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\ell_{i}\pi_{ei},\quad{\vec{\ell}}_{g}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}({\vec{\ell}}_{g})_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i},\quad{\vec{R}}_{g}.\pi_{ej}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}R_{ij}{\vec{e}}_{i},

(F.5)

i.e.
[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[g_{ij}]
,
[\ell]_{|\pi_{e}}=\pmatrix{\ell_{1}&...&\ell_{n}}
(row matrix),

[{\vec{\ell}}_{g}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{({\vec{\ell}}_{g})_{1}\cr\vdots\cr({\vec{\ell}}_{g})_{n}}
(column matrix),

[{\vec{R}}_{g}]_{\pi_{e},{\vec{e}}}=[R_{ij}]
.

(Duality notations:
\ell={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\ell_{i}e^{i}
,
{\vec{\ell}}_{g}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\ell_{g}^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,

{\vec{R}}_{g}.e^{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}R^{ij}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,
[{\vec{R}}_{g}]_{e,{\vec{e}}}=[R^{ij}]
.)

Proposition F.3

\framebox{$\displaystyle[{\vec{\ell}}_{g}]=[g]^{-1}.[\ell]^{T}$}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle[{\vec{R}}_{g}]=[g]^{-1}$},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad({\vec{\ell}}_{g})_{i}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}([g]^{-1})_{ij}(\ell)_{j}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}({\vec{R}}_{g})_{ij}(\ell)_{j}.

(F.6)

Full matrix notation:

[{\vec{\ell}}_{g}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=([g]_{|{\vec{e}}})^{-1}.([\ell]_{|\pi_{e}})^{T}
,
and

[{\vec{R}}_{g}]_{|\pi_{e},{\vec{e}}}=([g]_{|{\vec{e}}})^{-1}
.

Duality notation to see the change of variance induced by
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
(bottom index for
\ell
, top index for
{\vec{\ell}}_{g}
):

\ell_{g}^{i}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}R^{ij}\ell_{j},

(F.7)

i.e.
\ell_{g}^{i}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}g^{ij}\ell_{j}
when
([g]^{-1})_{ij}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}[g^{ij}]
.

(In particular, if
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-orthonormal basis, then
[{\vec{R}}_{g}]=[g]^{-1}=I
and
\ell_{g}^{i}=\ell_{i}
.)

Proof.
(
F.2
) gives

[\ell]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[{\vec{\ell}}_{g}]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}.[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}
for all
{\vec{x}}
,
thus
[\ell]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[{\vec{\ell}}_{g}]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}.[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}
, thus

[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{\ell}}_{g}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[\ell]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}
(since
[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}
), thus

[{\vec{\ell}}_{g}]=[g]^{-1}.[\ell]^{T}
.

And
{\vec{R}}_{g}.\ell\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqJg})}{\vec{\ell}}_{g}
gives

{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}(\ell)_{j}{\vec{R}}_{g}.\pi_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}({\vec{\ell}}_{g})_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
, thus

{\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}(\ell)_{j}R_{ij}{\vec{e}}_{i}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}({\vec{\ell}}_{g})_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
, thus

{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}R_{ij}(\ell)_{j}=({\vec{\ell}}_{g})_{i}
for all
i
, thus
[{\vec{R}}_{g}].[\ell]^{T}=[{\vec{\ell}}_{g}]
. Thus
[{\vec{R}}_{g}]=[g]^{-1}
.

Remark F.4

If a chosen inner dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
is imposed (e.g. Euclidean foot based)
and if duality notations are used,
then a usual notation for
{\vec{\ell}}_{g}
is
\ell^{\sharp}
,
since
{\vec{\ell}}_{g}={\vec{R}}_{g}(\ell)={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\ell^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
with a top index for
\ell^{i}
: the index
i
has been raised through
{\vec{R}}_{g}
.
Then (
F.2
) and (
F.6
) read (isometric framework)

\ell.{\vec{x}}={\ell^{\sharp}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{\vec{x}}\quad\hbox{and}\quad[{\ell^{\sharp}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{-1}.[\ell]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}.

(F.8)

We won’t use this notation (we deal with objectivity).

F.4
Change of Riesz representation vector, and Euclidean case

For one linear form
\ell\in E^{*}
, two observers with their inner dot products
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}

get two Riesz representation vectors
{\vec{\ell}}_{g}={\vec{R}}_{g}(\ell)
and
{\vec{\ell}}_{h}={\vec{R}}_{h}(\ell)
given by, cf. (
F.2
):

\forall{\vec{x}}\in E,\quad({\vec{\ell}}_{g},{\vec{x}})_{g}=\ell.{\vec{x}}=({\vec{\ell}}_{h},{\vec{x}})_{h}.

(F.9)

Proposition F.5

For any basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
in
E
, we have the change of representation vector formula:

[h]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{\ell}}_{h}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{\ell}}_{g}]_{|{\vec{e}}},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad[{\vec{\ell}}_{h}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[h]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{-1}.[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{\ell}}_{g}]_{|{\vec{e}}}.

(F.10)

In particular (for the Euclidean case), with
\lambda>0
:

\hbox{If }\;(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}=\lambda^{2}(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}\quad\hbox{then}\quad{\vec{\ell}}_{h}=\lambda^{2}{\vec{\ell}}_{g}.

(F.11)

Conversely, if
{\vec{\ell}}_{h}=\lambda^{2}{\vec{\ell}}_{g}
for all linear forms
\ell\in E^{*}
, then
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}=\lambda^{2}(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
.

So, a linear form
\ell
can
not
be identified with a Riesz representation vector (which one:
{\vec{\ell}}_{g}
?
{\vec{\ell}}_{h}
?);
In other words, a Riesz representation vector
{\vec{R}}_{g}(\ell)
is not objective, is not intrinsic to a linear form
\ell
.

NB: (
F.10
)-(
F.11
) is a “change of vector formula” (one linear form gives two vectors relative to two inner dot products); It is not a “change of basis formula” (for one vector and its two sets of components).

Proof.
(
F.9
)
gives
[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}.[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{\ell}}_{g}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}.[h]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{\ell}}_{h}]_{|{\vec{e}}}

for all
{\vec{x}}
, hence
[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{\ell}}_{g}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[h]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{\ell}}_{h}]_{|{\vec{e}}}
, i.e. (
F.10
).

In particular
\lambda^{2}(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}=(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
give

\lambda^{2}({\vec{\ell}}_{g},{\vec{x}})_{h}=({\vec{\ell}}_{g},{\vec{x}})_{g}\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqrtr2})}({\vec{\ell}}_{h},{\vec{x}})_{h}
for all
{\vec{x}}
, hence
\lambda^{2}{\vec{\ell}}_{g}={\vec{\ell}}_{h}
.

Converse:
\lambda^{2}{\vec{\ell}}_{g}={\vec{\ell}}_{h}
for all
\ell
gives

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}({\vec{\ell}}_{g},{\vec{x}})_{h}=({\vec{\ell}}_{h},{\vec{x}})_{h}\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqrtr2})}({\vec{\ell}}_{g},{\vec{x}})_{g}
, for all
{\vec{x}}
and for all
\ell
, thus for all
{\vec{\ell}}_{g}

thanks to the isomorphism
{\vec{R}}_{g}:E^{*}\rightarrow E
, cf. (
F.3
), thus

\lambda^{2}(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}=(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
.

Example F.6

If
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
are the Euclidean dot products made with the foot and the metre then, with (
F.9
),

(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}=\lambda^{2}(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad{\vec{\ell}}_{h}=\lambda^{2}{\vec{\ell}}_{g},\quad\hbox{with}\quad\lambda^{2}>10:

(F.12)

{\vec{\ell}}_{g}
(English) and
{\vec{\ell}}_{h}
(French) are quite different! A Riesz representation vector is subjective, and certainly not “canonical” (a word that you may find in books where… nothing is defined…).

E.g., aviation:
If you do want to use a Riesz representation vector to represent a
\ell\in{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{*}}
,
it is vital to know which Euclidean dot product is in use, see also remark
A.14
(Mars Climate Orbiter Crash).
Recall: The foot is the international unit of altitude for aviation.

Example F.7

If
f\in C^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}};\mathbb{R})
and
p\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
, the differential of
f
at
p
is the linear form
df(p)\in{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{*}}
defined by, for all
{\vec{w}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
,

df(p).{\vec{w}}:=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{f(p+h{\vec{w}})-f(p)\over h}\quad\hbox{(definition independent of any inner dot product)},

(F.13)

see (
S.5
).
If you can choose an inner dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
then the gradient
{\vec{\rm grad}}_{g}f(p)
is the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Riesz representation vector of
df(p)
:

{\vec{\rm grad}}_{g}f(p):={\vec{R}}_{g}(df(p)),\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad df(p).{\vec{w}}={\vec{\rm grad}}_{g}f(p)\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,{\vec{w}},\;\;\forall{\vec{w}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}.

(F.14)

And (
F.12
) gives

{\vec{\rm grad}}_{h}f(p)=\lambda^{2}{\vec{\rm grad}}_{g}f(p)\quad\hbox{with}\quad\lambda^{2}>10\;\hbox{ (English vs French)}:

(F.15)

The gradient is very dependent on the observer (the gradient is subjective, the differential is objective).

Remark F.8

The “gradient” is observer dependent; We already had this observer dependence for the usual derivative in the 1-D case
f:x\in\mathbb{R}\rightarrow f(x)\in\mathbb{R}
; Question: What does
f^{\prime}(x)=3
mean?

Answer.
11- For one observer, it means
f^{\prime}(x)=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{f(x+h)-f(x)\over h}
… but… where in the departure space this observer has chosen a basis vector
{\vec{a}}
of length
1
for him (e.g. length 1 foot) which he calls
1
; So, with no abusive notations, his derivative
f^{\prime}(x)
is in fact

f_{a}^{\prime}(x)=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{f(x+h{\vec{a}})-f(x)\over h}
.

12- For some other observer, it means
f^{\prime}(x)=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{f(x+h)-f(x)\over h}
… but… where in the departure space this observer has chosen a basis vector
{\vec{b}}
of length
1
for him (e.g. length 1 metre) which he calls
1
; So, with no abusive notations, his derivative
f^{\prime}(x)
is in fact

f_{b}^{\prime}(x)=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{f(x+h{\vec{b}})-f(x)\over h}
.

13- Both observer use the same formula
f^{\prime}(x)=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{f(x+h)-f(x)\over h}
but get different results! Indeed, if
{\vec{b}}=\lambda{\vec{a}}
, then

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{f(x+h{\vec{b}})-f(x)\over h}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{f(x+h\lambda{\vec{a}})-f(x)\over h}=\lambda\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{f(x+(h\lambda){\vec{a}})-f(x)\over(h\lambda)}=\lambda\lim_{k\rightarrow 0}{f(x+k{\vec{a}})-f(x)\over k}
thus

f_{b}^{\prime}(x)=\lambda f_{a}^{\prime}(x),\quad\hbox{with}\quad\lambda\simeq 3.28

(F.16)

with foot and metre… Quite different results!
(In fact
f^{\prime}(x)={\hbox{opposite side}\over\hbox{adjacent side}}
depends on the length of the adjacent side…)

Remark F.9

We insist on the subjectivity of the gradient:

20- The differential of
f
at a point
x
along a vector
{\vec{w}}\in\vec{\mathbb{R}}
is

df(x).{\vec{w}}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{f(x+h{\vec{w}})-f(x)\over h}
and is objective: The observers all use this same formula.

21- An observer chooses a Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
(e.g. based on the foot), then represent
df(x)
by its
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Riesz representation vector
{\vec{R}}_{g}(df(x)){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{\rm grad}}_{g}f(x)
called the gradient of
f
at
x
relative to
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
.

22- Another observer chooses a Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
(e.g. based on the metre), then represent
df(x)
by its
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
-Riesz representation vector
{\vec{R}}_{h}(df(x)){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{\rm grad}}_{h}f(x)
called the gradient of
f
at
x
relative to
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
.

23- Both observer use the same formula
df(x).{\vec{w}}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{f(x+h{\vec{w}})-f(x)\over h}
to get
a different result:
{\vec{\rm grad}}_{h}f=\lambda^{2}{\vec{\rm grad}}_{g}f
, because they use different measuring tools
(one based on the foot, the other on the metre).

24- Recall: The gradient depends on a choice of a Euclidean unit.

Exercice F.10

In (
F.16
) we have
f_{b}^{\prime}(x)=\lambda f_{a}^{\prime}(x)
. And the 1-D gradient gives

{\rm grad}_{b}f(x)=\lambda^{2}{\rm grad}_{a}f(x)
. Why?

Answer
. To define a gradient
{\rm grad}_{a}f
we need a Euclidean dots products
(\cdot,\cdot)_{a}
built from a basis
({\vec{a}})
in
\vec{\mathbb{R}}
, while to define
f_{a}^{\prime}
we need a unit of length. Details:

({\vec{a}})
and
({\vec{b}})
are two bases in
\vec{\mathbb{R}}
with
{\vec{b}}=\lambda{\vec{a}}
, thus
(\cdot,\cdot)_{a}=\lambda^{2}(\cdot,\cdot)_{b}

(since
1=({\vec{a}},{\vec{a}})_{a}=({\vec{b}},{\vec{b}})_{b}=(\lambda{\vec{a}},\lambda{\vec{a}})_{b}=\lambda^{2}({\vec{a}},{\vec{a}})_{b}
gives

({\vec{a}},{\vec{a}})_{a}=\lambda^{2}({\vec{a}},{\vec{a}})_{b}
and
({\vec{a}})
is a basis).
And we have
f_{b}^{\prime}(x)\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqfpab})}\lambda f_{a}^{\prime}(x)
,
i.e.
df(x).{\vec{b}}=\lambda df(x).{\vec{a}}
,
thus
({\rm grad}f_{b}(x),{\vec{b}})_{b}=\lambda({\rm grad}f_{a}(x),{\vec{a}})_{a}
,
thus
({\rm grad}f_{b}(x),{\vec{b}})_{b}=\lambda\lambda^{2}({\rm grad}f_{a}(x),{{\vec{b}}\over\lambda})_{b}=(\lambda^{2}{\rm grad}f_{a}(x),{\vec{b}})_{b}
,
thus
{\rm grad}f_{b}(x)=\lambda^{2}{\rm grad}f_{a}(x)
.

Exercice F.11

1- Prove that
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}=\lambda^{2}(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
gives
||{\vec{\ell}}_{h}||_{g}=\lambda||{\vec{\ell}}_{h}||_{h}
.
2- Does it contradict the Riesz representation theorem which gives
||\ell||_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{*}}=||{\vec{\ell}}_{g}||_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
?

Answer
. 1-
{\vec{\ell}}_{h}\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqfcvvr3})}\lambda^{2}{\vec{\ell}}_{g}
gives

||{\vec{\ell}}_{h}||_{h}=\lambda^{2}||{\vec{\ell}}_{g}||_{h}=\lambda||{\vec{\ell}}_{g}||_{g}
since
||.||_{h}=\lambda||.||_{g}
.

2- No, since
||\ell||_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{*}}:=\sup_{||{\vec{x}}||_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}=1}|\ell.{\vec{x}}|
depends on the norm
||.||_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
chosen;
Here
||.||_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
is either
||.||_{g}
or
||.||_{h}
. Thus if you write
||\ell||_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{*}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}||\ell||_{g*}

if you use the norme
||.||_{g}
, then

||\ell||_{h*}=\sup_{{\vec{v}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}}{|\ell.{\vec{v}}|\over||{\vec{v}}||_{h}}=\sup_{{\vec{v}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}}{|\ell.{\vec{v}}|\over{1\over\lambda}||{\vec{v}}||_{g}}=\lambda\sup_{{\vec{v}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}}{|\ell.{\vec{v}}|\over||{\vec{v}}||_{g}}=\lambda||\ell||_{g*}
.

F.5
A Riesz representation vector is contravariant

{\vec{\ell}}_{g}
is a vector in
E
, cf. (
F.2
), so it is contravariant. To be convinced:

Exercice F.12

Check:

[{\vec{\ell}}_{g}]_{|{new}}=P^{-1}.[{\vec{\ell}}_{g}]_{|{old}}\quad\hbox{(contravariance formula)}.

(F.17)

Answer
. Consider two bases
({{\vec{e}}_{old,i}})
and
({{\vec{e}}_{new,i}})
in
E
.
With the change of basis formulas
[{\vec{x}}]_{|{new}}=P^{-1}.[{\vec{x}}]_{|{old}}
and
[g]_{|{new}}=P^{T}.[g]_{|{old}}.P
, (
F.2
) gives (with (
A.94
)), for all
{\vec{x}}
,

\displaystyle{[{\vec{x}}]_{|{old}}^{T}.[g]_{|{old}}.[{\vec{\ell}}_{g}]_{|{old}}=\ell.{\vec{x}}=}

\displaystyle{{}[{\vec{x}}]_{|{new}}^{T}.[g]_{|{new}}.[{\vec{\ell}}_{g}]_{|{new}}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}([{\vec{x}}]_{|{old}}^{T}.P^{-T}).(P^{T}.[g]_{|{old}}.P).[{\vec{\ell}}_{g}]_{|{new}}=[{\vec{x}}]_{|{old}}^{T}.[g]_{|{old}}.(P.[{\vec{\ell}}_{g}]_{|{new}}),}

(F.18)

thus
[{\vec{\ell}}_{g}]_{|{old}}=P.[{\vec{\ell}}_{g}]_{|{new}}
since
[g]
is invertible (an inner dot product is positive definite), thus (
F.17
).

Remark F.13

\bullet
Dont forget: A representation vector
{\vec{\ell}}_{g}
is not intrinsic to the linear form
\ell
because it depends on a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
(depends on a observer: foot? metre?)).
Reminder: there is no natural canonical isomorphism between
E
and
E^{*}
, i.e. it is impossible to identify a linear form with a vector, see §
T.2
.

\bullet

{\vec{\ell}}_{g}
is incompatible with the use of push-forwards,
cf. §
7.2
.

\bullet

{\vec{\ell}}_{g}
is incompatible with the use of Lie derivatives, cf. (
9.51
).

F.6
What is a vector versus a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-vector?

1- Originally, a vector was a bipoint vector
{\vec{v}}=\overrightarrow{AB}
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}
used to represent of a “material object”. E.g. the height of a child is represented on a wall by a vertical bipoint vector
{\vec{x}}
starting from the ground up to a pencil line.
The vector
{\vec{x}}
is objective: Any observer uses this same vector to get the height of the child…
and then use “their subjective unit” (foot, metre…) to give a value.

2- Then (mid 19th century), the concept of vector space was introduced:
It is a quadruplet
(E,+,K,.)
where
+
is an inner law,
(E,+)
is a group,
K
is a field,
.
is a external law on
E
(called a scalar multiplication) compatible with
+
(see any math book).

And then the concept of scalar inner dot product (in a vector space) was introduced.

3- We can then get non “material” vectors (“subjectively built vectors”).
E.g.: start with our usual vector space
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
of bi-point vectors, then
consider its dual
({{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{*}},+,\mathbb{R},.){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{*}}
. Then, for a given
\ell\in{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{*}}
(a given measuring device), consider two observers:
An English observer with his foot built Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
, and a French observer with with his metre built Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
. These observers build their own artificial Riesz representation vectors
{\vec{\ell}}_{g}={\vec{R}}_{g}(\ell)\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
and
{\vec{\ell}}_{h}={\vec{R}}_{h}(\ell)
, cf (
F.12
); They remark that
{\vec{\ell}}_{g}\neq{\vec{\ell}}_{h}
:
These constructions are very subjective.

4- Then, with differential geometry, a vector
{\vec{v}}
has been redefined: It is a “tangent vector”, which means that there exists a
C^{1}
curve
c:s\in[a,b]\rightarrow c(s)\in E

such that
{\vec{v}}
is defined at a
p=c(s)\in{\rm Im}(c)
by

{\vec{v}}(p):={\vec{c}\,^{\prime}}(s)
(so a vector is part of a vector field, here defined along the range of
c
). (This definition of a tangent vector is applicable to “tangent vectors to a surface”
i.e. tangent vectors to a manifold, see e.g. §
9.1.1
,2-.) Then it is shown that
{\vec{v}}
is equivalent to
{\partial\over\partial{\vec{v}}}=
the directional derivative in the direction
{\vec{v}}
(natural canonical isomorphism between
E
and
{E^{**}}
). For other equivalent definitions of vectors, see Abraham–Marsden
[
3
]
.

F.7
The “
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-dual vectorial bases” of one basis (and warnings)

Framework:
E
is a finite dimensional vector space,
\dim E=n
(e.g.
E={\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}
).
An observer chooses an inner dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}

(e.g., in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}
, a foot-built Euclidean dot product).
Hence the results will be subjective. And
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is some basis in
E
.

F.7.1
A basis and its many associated “dual vectorial basis”

Definition F.14

The
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-dual vectorial basis (or
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-vectorial dual basis, or
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-dual basis)
of the basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is the basis
({{\vec{e}}_{ig}})
in
E
defined by

\forall j=1,...,n,\quad({{\vec{e}}_{ig}},{\vec{e}}_{j})_{g}=\delta_{ij},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle{{\vec{e}}_{ig}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,{\vec{e}}_{j}=\delta_{ij}$}.

(F.19)

NB: A vectorial dual basis is not unique: It depends on the chosen inner dot product, see e.g. (
F.22
).

NB: Pay attention to the notations:
{{\vec{e}}_{ig}}
is a contravariant vector (
{{\vec{e}}_{ig}}\in E
), so, even if you use the Einstein convention, the index
i
in
{{\vec{e}}_{ig}}
must be a bottom index.

Let
(\pi_{ei})
be the (covariant) dual basis of the basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
,
i.e. the
\pi_{ei}\in E^{*}
are the objective (the same for all observers) linear forms defined by
\pi_{ei}.{\vec{e}}_{j}=\delta_{ij}
for all
j
, cf. (
A.7
).

Definition F.15
(Equivalent definition.)

The
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-dual vectorial basis of the basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})

is the basis
({{\vec{e}}_{ig}})
in
E
made of the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Riesz representative vectors of the
\pi_{ei}
, i.e.

\framebox{$\displaystyle{{\vec{e}}_{ig}}:={\vec{R}}_{g}(\pi_{ei})$},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad{{\vec{e}}_{ig}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,{\vec{v}}=\pi_{ei}.{\vec{v}},\;\;\forall{\vec{v}}\in E.

(F.20)

where
{\vec{R}}_{g}
is the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Riesz operator, see (
F.3
).

With duality notations,
(e^{i})
is the dual basis and
{{\vec{e}}_{ig}}:={\vec{R}}_{g}(e^{i})
, i.e.
({{\vec{e}}_{ig}},{\vec{v}})_{g}=e^{i}.{\vec{v}}
for all
{\vec{v}}\in E
where here the position of the index
i

is bottom on the left and up on the right, since
{\vec{R}}_{g}
changes a covariant vector (a linear form) into a contravariant vector.

Exercice F.16

Prove that the vectors
{{\vec{e}}_{ig}}
satisfy the contravariant change of basis formula

[{{\vec{e}}_{ig}}]_{|{new}}=P^{-1}.[{{\vec{e}}_{ig}}]_{|{old}}

(F.21)

(the
{{\vec{e}}_{jg}}
are indeed “contravariant vectors”).

Answer
.
\bullet
First answer:
{{\vec{e}}_{ig}}
is a vector in
E
, thus it is contravariant.

\bullet
Second answer: Apply (
F.17
) since
{{\vec{e}}_{ig}}
is a Riesz-representation vector.

\bullet
Third answer = direct computation: Consider two bases
({\vec{a}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
, and the transition matrix
P
from
({\vec{a}}_{i})
to
({\vec{b}}_{i})
, i.e.,

{\vec{b}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}P_{ij}{\vec{a}}_{i}
for all
j
.
(
F.19
)
and the change of basis formulas for the vectors
{\vec{e}}_{i}
and the bilinear form
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
give

[{\vec{e}}_{j}]_{|{\vec{a}}}^{T}.[g]_{|{\vec{a}}}.[{{\vec{e}}_{ig}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}=({{\vec{e}}_{ig}},{\vec{e}}_{j})_{g}=[{\vec{e}}_{j}]_{|{\vec{b}}}^{T}.[g]_{|{\vec{b}}}.[{{\vec{e}}_{ig}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}=(P^{-1}.[{\vec{e}}_{j}]_{|{\vec{a}}})^{T}.(P^{T}.[g]_{|{\vec{a}}}.P).[{{\vec{e}}_{ig}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}=[{\vec{e}}_{j}]_{|{\vec{a}}}^{T}.[g]_{|{\vec{a}}}.P.[{{\vec{e}}_{ig}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}
for all
i,j
, thus
[{{\vec{e}}_{ig}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}=P.[{{\vec{e}}_{ig}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}
, thus (
F.21
).

Exercice F.17

Consider two inner dot products
(\cdot,\cdot)_{a}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{b}
(e.g., a foot-built and a metre-built Euclidean dot product),
and a basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
in
E
. Call
({{\vec{e}}_{ia}})
and
({{\vec{e}}_{ib}})
the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{a}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{b}
-dual vectorial bases.
Prove:

(\cdot,\cdot)_{a}=\lambda^{2}(\cdot,\cdot)_{b}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad{\vec{e}}_{ib}=\lambda^{2}{\vec{e}}_{ia},\quad\forall i.

(F.22)

E.g.,
\lambda^{2}>10
with foot and metre built Euclidean bases:
{\vec{e}}_{ib}
is very different from
{\vec{e}}_{ia}\,
!
A dual vectorial basis highly depends on an observer: A vectorial dual basis is
not
intrinsic to
({\vec{e}}_{i})

(
not
objective).

Answer
. (
F.19
) gives

({{\vec{e}}_{ib}},{\vec{e}}_{j})_{b}=\delta_{ij}=({{\vec{e}}_{ia}},{\vec{e}}_{j})_{a}=\lambda^{2}({{\vec{e}}_{ia}},{\vec{e}}_{j})_{b}
,
thus
({{\vec{e}}_{ib}}-\lambda^{2}{{\vec{e}}_{ia}},{\vec{e}}_{j})_{b}=\delta_{ij}
, for all
i,j
.

Example F.18

If
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-orthonormal basis we trivially get
{{\vec{e}}_{ig}}={\vec{e}}_{i}
for all
i
, i.e.,
({{\vec{e}}_{ig}})=({\vec{e}}_{i})
.This particular case is not compatible with joint work by an English (foot) and French (metre) observer.

F.7.2
Components of
{{\vec{e}}_{jg}}
in the basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})

Proposition F.19

The components of
{{\vec{e}}_{jg}}
in the basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
are given by, for any
j\in[1,n]_{\mathbb{N}}
,

\framebox{$\displaystyle[{{\vec{e}}_{jg}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[{\vec{R}}_{g}]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{e}}_{j}]_{|{\vec{e}}}$}=([g]_{|{\vec{e}}})^{-1}.[{\vec{e}}_{j}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\;\hbox{the $j$-th column of\penalty\ $([g]_{|{\vec{e}}})^{-1}$},

(F.23)

i.e. the
i
-th component of
{{\vec{e}}_{jg}}
is
([g]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{-1})_{ij}
.

Thus the matrix of
g(\cdot,\cdot)
in the basis
({{\vec{e}}_{ig}})
is the inverse of the matrix of
g(\cdot,\cdot)
in the basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
:

([g({{\vec{e}}_{ig}},{{\vec{e}}_{jg}})]=)\quad[g]_{|({{\vec{e}}_{ig}})}=[g]_{|({\vec{e}}_{i})}{}^{-1}\quad(=([g({\vec{e}}_{i},{\vec{e}}_{j})])^{-1}).

(F.24)

Proof.
First proof of (
F.23
) (straight forward calculation): (
F.19
) gives, for all
i,j
,

[{\vec{e}}_{j}]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}.[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{{\vec{e}}_{ig}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\delta_{ij}=[{\vec{e}}_{j}]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}.[{\vec{e}}_{i}]_{|{\vec{e}}},\quad\hbox{thus}\quad[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{{\vec{e}}_{ig}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[{\vec{e}}_{i}]_{|{\vec{e}}}.

(F.25)

Second proof of (
F.23
): Apply (
F.6
) (generic Riesz representation result) to get (
F.23
).

Thus,
[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}
being symmetric we have
[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}{}^{-1}
symmetric, and
g({{\vec{e}}_{ig}},{{\vec{e}}_{jg}})=[{{\vec{e}}_{ig}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}.[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{{\vec{e}}_{jg}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[{\vec{e}}_{i}]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}.[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}{}^{-1}.[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}{}^{-1}.[{\vec{e}}_{j}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[{\vec{e}}_{i}]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}.[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}{}^{-1}.[{\vec{e}}_{j}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=([g]_{|{\vec{e}}}{}^{-1})_{ij}
, thus (
F.24
).

Example F.20

{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{2}}}
,
[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{1&0\cr 0&2}
, thus
[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{-1}=\pmatrix{1&0\cr 0&{1\over 2}}
.
Thus
{\vec{e}}_{1g}={\vec{e}}_{1}
,
{\vec{e}}_{2g}={1\over 2}{\vec{e}}_{2}
.

Remark F.21

Warning:
When
([g]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{-1})_{ij}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}g^{ij}
then (
F.23
) reads

{{\vec{e}}_{jg}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}g^{ij}{\vec{e}}_{i},

(F.26)

where the Einstein convention is
not
satisfied:
The Einstein convention is satisfied with

{{\vec{e}}_{jg}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}({{\vec{e}}_{jg}})^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}(P_{j})^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}

(F.27)

(the components of vectors have up indices),
and this can be verified with
({{\vec{e}}_{ig}},{\vec{e}}_{j})_{g}\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdefbdv})}\delta_{ij}
which gives
{\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{n}}(P_{i})^{k}g_{kj}=\delta_{ij}
.
And in (
F.26
)
the scalars
g^{ij}
is just another name for
(P_{j})^{i}
, nothing more (nothing to do with the Einstein convention).

We insist:In other words:
M=[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[M_{ij}]
is a matrix, and its inverse is the matrix
M^{-1}=[M_{ij}]^{-1}
:
A matrix is just a collection of scalars (has nothing to do with the Einstein convention), and its inverse is also a collection of scalars, and you do not change this fact
by calling
M^{-1}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}[M^{ij}]
(the use of up indices is irrelevant for matrices). See remark
A.50
.

And because
(P_{j})^{i}
equals
([g]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{-1})_{ij}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}g^{ij}
, some people rename
{{\vec{e}}_{jg}}
as
{\vec{e}}^{\,j}
… to get
{\vec{e}}^{\,j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}g^{ij}{\vec{e}}_{i}
…
But doing so they
despise
Einstein’s convention, despite eventual claims: They confuse covariance and contravariance… and add confusion to the confusion.

NB: Recall: If in trouble with a notation which comes as a surprise (the notation
g^{ij}
here), use classical notations: Then no misuse of Einstein’s convention and no possible misinterpretation.
In particular here
{{\vec{e}}_{jg}}
is a (contravariant) vector.

F.7.3
Multiple admissible notations for the components of
{{\vec{e}}_{jg}}

Let
{\cal P}\in{\cal L}(E;E)
be the change of basis endomorphism from
({\vec{e}}_{i})
to
({{\vec{e}}_{ig}})
: defined by

{\cal P}.{\vec{e}}_{j}={{\vec{e}}_{jg}}
.
And let
P=[{\cal P}]_{|{\vec{e}}}
(the associated transition matrix).
It gives multiple admissible (non confusing) notations for the components of
{{\vec{e}}_{jg}}
relative to the basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
:

{{\vec{e}}_{jg}}={\cal P}.{\vec{e}}_{j}=\underbrace{{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}P_{ij}{\vec{e}}_{i}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}(P_{j})_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}}_{\hbox{\footnotesize clas{.}}}=\underbrace{{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}(P_{j})^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}{P^{i}{}_{j}}{\vec{e}}_{i}}_{\hbox{\footnotesize dual}},

(F.28)

i.e. the
i
-th component of the vector
{{\vec{e}}_{jg}}
has the names
P_{ij}=(P_{j})_{i}=(P_{j})^{i}={P^{i}{}_{j}}
,
i.e.
P=[{\cal P}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[P_{ij}]=[(P_{j})_{i}]=[(P_{j})^{i}]=[{P^{i}{}_{j}}]
(four different notations for the same matrix), i.e.

\forall j,\quad[{{\vec{e}}_{jg}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[{\cal P}]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{e}}_{j}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{P_{1j}\cr\vdots\cr P_{nj}}=\pmatrix{(P_{j})_{1}\cr\vdots\cr(P_{j})_{n}}=\pmatrix{P^{1}{}_{j}\cr\vdots\cr P^{n}{}_{j}}=\pmatrix{(P_{j})^{1}\cr\vdots\cr(P_{j})^{n}}

(F.29)

= the
j
-th column of
[{\cal P}]_{|{\vec{e}}}
.
You can choose any notation, depending on your current need or mood…

F.7.4
(Huge) differences between “the (covariant) dual basis” and “a dual vectorial basis”

1.

A basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
has an infinite number of vectorial dual bases
({{\vec{e}}_{ig}})
,
as many as the number of inner dot products
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
(as many as observers), see (
F.23
).

While a basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
has a unique intrinsic (covariant) dual basis
\displaystyle(\pi_{ei}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}(e^{i})
, cf. (
A.7
): Two observers who consider the same basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
have the same (covariant) dual basis.

2.

\pi_{ei}=e^{i}
is covariant, while
{\vec{e}}_{i}
and
{{\vec{e}}_{ig}}
are contravariant.
And there is no transition matrix between
({\vec{e}}_{i})
and
(\pi_{ei})=(e^{i})
,
since
{\vec{e}}_{i}\in E
and
\pi_{ei}=e^{i}\in E^{*}
don’t live in the same vector space.

3.

If you fly, it is vital to use the dual basis
(\pi_{ei})=(e^{i})
: It is possibly fatal if you confuse foot and metre at takeoff and at landing (if you survived takeoff) because of the choice of different Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
or
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
;
See e.g. the Mars Climate Orbiter crash, remark
A.14
. Einstein’s convention can help… only if it is really followed.

F.7.5
About the notation
g^{ij}
= shorthand notation for
(g^{\sharp})^{ij}

Definition F.22

The Riesz associated inner dot product
g^{\sharp}\in{\cal L}(E^{*},E^{*};\mathbb{R})

is the bilinear form defined by, for all
\ell,m\in E^{*}
,

g^{\sharp}(\ell,m):=g({\vec{\ell}}_{g},\vec{m}_{g}),\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad(\ell,m)_{g^{\sharp}}:=({\vec{\ell}}_{g},\vec{m}_{g})_{g}.

(F.30)

where
{\vec{\ell}}_{g}={\vec{R}}_{g}(\ell)
and
\vec{m}_{g}={\vec{R}}_{g}(m)
.

Thus
g^{\sharp}(\cdot,\cdot){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}(\cdot,\cdot)_{g^{\sharp}}
is indeed an inner dot product in
E^{*}
: trivial check.

Quantification:

({\vec{e}}_{i})
is a basis in
E
and
(e^{i})
is its dual basis (duality notations).
(
F.30
) gives:

(g^{\sharp})^{ij}:=g^{\sharp}(e^{i},e^{j})=g({{\vec{e}}_{ig}},{{\vec{e}}_{jg}}),\quad\hbox{thus}\quad[g^{\sharp}]_{|e}=\framebox{$\displaystyle[(g^{\sharp})^{ij}]=[g_{ij}]^{-1}$}=[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{-1},

(F.31)

cf. (
F.24
).
And

\framebox{$\displaystyle[(g^{\sharp})^{ij}]\;\mathop{=}^{\rm shorthand}_{\rm notation}\;[g^{ij}]$}.

(F.32)

Classical notations:
[g^{\sharp}]_{|e}=[(g^{\sharp})_{ij}]=[g^{\sharp}(\pi_{ei},\pi_{ej})]=[g({{\vec{e}}_{ig}},{{\vec{e}}_{jg}})]=[g_{ij}]^{-1}=([g]_{|{\vec{e}}})^{-1}
.

Exercice F.23

How do we compute
g^{\sharp}(\ell,m)
with matrix computations?

Answer
.
\ell={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\ell_{i}e^{i}
and
m={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}m_{j}e^{j}
give

g^{\sharp}(\ell,m)={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}\ell_{i}m_{j}g^{\sharp}(e^{i},e^{j})={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}\ell_{i}(g^{\sharp})^{ij}m_{j}=[\ell]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[g^{\sharp}]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[m]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}=[\ell]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{-1}.[m]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}

(a linear form is represented by a row matrix,).

Exercice F.24

(
F.30
) tells that the
{2\choose 0}
tensor
g^{\sharp}\in{\cal L}(E^{*},E^{*};\mathbb{R})
was created from the
{0\choose 2}
tensor
g=(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}\in{\cal L}(E,E;\mathbb{R})
using twice the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Riesz representation theorem.
