1- Show that if you use the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Riesz representation theorem just once you get the

{1\choose 1}
tensor
g^{\natural}\in{\cal L}(E^{*},E;\mathbb{R})\simeq{\cal L}(E;E)
given by

g^{\natural}=I.

(F.33)

2- Reciprocal: What is the
{0\choose 2}
tensor
g^{\flat}\in{\cal L}(E,E;\mathbb{R})
that you create from the identity
I\in{\cal L}(E;E)
when using the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Riesz representation theorem once?

3- Summary:
\widetilde{I}=g^{\natural}
gives
(\widetilde{I})^{\flat}=g^{\flat}=g
and
(\widetilde{I})^{\sharp}=g^{\sharp}

Answer
. 1-
g^{\natural}\in{\cal L}(E^{*},E;\mathbb{R})
is defined by
g^{\natural}(\ell,{\vec{w}})=({\vec{\ell}}_{g},{\vec{w}})_{g}

for all
(\ell,{\vec{w}})\in E^{*}\times E
, where
{\vec{\ell}}_{g}
is the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Riesz representation vector of
\ell
. Thus
g^{\natural}(\ell,{\vec{w}})=\ell.{\vec{w}}=\ell.I.{\vec{w}}
, for all
(\ell,{\vec{w}})\in E^{*}\times E
, hence

g^{\natural}\in{\cal L}(E^{*},E;\mathbb{R})
is naturally canonically associated with the identity
I\in{\cal L}(E;E)
.

2- The identity operator
I\in{\cal L}(E;E)
(observer independent) is naturally canonically associated with the
{1\choose 1}
tensor
\widetilde{I}\in{\cal L}(E^{*},E;\mathbb{R})
defined by
\widetilde{I}(\ell,{\vec{w}})=\ell.I.{\vec{w}}=\ell.{\vec{w}}
for all
(\ell,{\vec{w}})\in E^{*}\times E
, thus
\widetilde{I}=g^{\natural}
.

Appendix G
Cauchy–Green deformation tensor
C=F^{T}.F

Framework:

{\widetilde{\Phi}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\mathbb{R}\times{\it O\!b\!j}&\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\cr(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})&\rightarrow{\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})\cr}}\,\right\}
is a motion of
{\it O\!b\!j}
,

{\Omega_{\tau}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}(\tau,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
is the configuration of
{\it O\!b\!j}
at any
\tau
,

{t_{0}}
and
t
are fixed,

\Phi:={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\Omega_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow{\Omega_{t}}\cr P&\rightarrow p=\Phi(P)\cr}}\,\right\}
is the associated motion between
{t_{0}}
and
t
,
and
F(P):=d\Phi(P):\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t_{0}}}}&\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}\cr{\vec{W}}&\rightarrow{\vec{w}}=F(P).{\vec{W}}:=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{\Phi(P{+}h{\vec{W}})-\Phi(P)\over h}\cr}}\,\right\}

is the deformation gradient at
P
between
{t_{0}}
and
t
,
cf. (
4.2
).

G.0
Goal

Construction of
C
(summary of Cauchy’s approach):

1- At
{t_{0}}
, consider two vectors
{\vec{W}}_{1}
and
{\vec{W}}_{2}
,

2- at
t
, they are distorted by the motion and become the vectors
F.{\vec{W}}_{1}
and
F.{\vec{W}}_{2}
;

3- Then choose a Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\cdot\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}\cdot
, the same at all
t
(to simplify);

4- Then, by definition of the transposed,
(F.{\vec{W}}_{1})\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}(F.{\vec{W}}_{2})=(F^{T}.F.{\vec{W}}_{1})\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{\vec{W}}_{2}
: You have got the Cauchy strain tensor
C:=F^{T}.F
;

5- Then
(F.{\vec{W}}_{1})\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}(F.{\vec{W}}_{2})-{{\vec{W}}_{1}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{{\vec{W}}_{2}}=((C{-}I).{\vec{W}}_{1})\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{\vec{W}}_{2}

gives a measure of the deformation with
{\vec{W}}_{2}
as a reference, measure that is used to build first order constitutive laws for the stress (Cauchy).

G.1
Transposed
F^{T}
: Inner dot products required

We first give the functional definition of
F^{T}
(qualitative);
Then we get the usual matrix representation of
F^{T}
relative to observers (quantification).

G.1.1
Definition of the function
F^{T}

At
{t_{0}}
, a past observer chose an inner dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
,
and at
t
the present observer chooses an inner dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
.

By definition,
the transposed of the linear map
F(P)\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})
relative to
(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
is the linear map
F(P)^{T}_{Gg}\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}})
defined by,
for all
{\vec{U}}_{P}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
(vector at
P
) and
{\vec{w}}_{p}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
(vector at
p
),

(F(P)^{T}_{Gg}.{\vec{w}}_{p},{\vec{U}}_{P})_{G}=(F(P).{\vec{U}}_{P},{\vec{w}}_{p})_{g},\quad\hbox{in short}\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle(F^{T}_{Gg}.{\vec{w}},{\vec{U}})_{G}=(F.{\vec{U}},{\vec{w}})_{g}$},

(G.1)

see (
A.68
).
This defines
F^{T}_{Gg}(p):=F(P)^{T}_{Gg}
when
p=\Phi(P)
:

F^{T}_{Gg}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\Omega_{t}}&\rightarrow{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}})\cr p&\rightarrow\framebox{$\displaystyle F^{T}_{Gg}(p):=F(P)^{T}_{Gg}$}\crcr}}\,\right\},\quad\hbox{so in short}\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle(F^{T}.{\vec{w}})\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!G$}}}\,{\vec{U}}={\vec{w}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,(F.{\vec{U}})$},

(G.2)

without forgetting that
F^{T}:=F^{T}_{Gg}
depends on
(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
.

Exercice G.1

1. In
F^{T}.{\vec{z}}.{\vec{W}}={\vec{z}}.F.{\vec{W}}=F.{\vec{W}}.{\vec{z}}={\vec{W}}.F^{T}.{\vec{z}}
, which dots are inner dot products?

2. What does
F.{\vec{W}}_{1}.F.{\vec{W}}_{2}={\vec{W}}_{1}.F^{T}.F.{\vec{W}}_{2}
mean?

Answer
. 1. No choice:

({\vec{W}},{\vec{z}})\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}\times{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
, so

(F^{T}.{\vec{z}})\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!G$}}}\,{\vec{W}}={\vec{z}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,(F.{\vec{W}})=(F.{\vec{W}})\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,{\vec{z}}={\vec{W}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!G$}}}\,(F^{T}.{\vec{z}})
.

2. No choice:
{\vec{W}}_{1},{\vec{W}}_{2}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
, so

(F.{\vec{W}}_{1})\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,(F.{\vec{W}}_{2})={\vec{W}}_{1}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!G$}}}\,(F^{T}.(F.{\vec{W}}_{2}))
.

Remark G.2

More generally, on a surface
\Omega
(a manifold), (
G.1
) is defined for all
({\vec{U}}_{P},{\vec{w}}_{p})\in T_{P}{\Omega_{t_{0}}}\times T_{p}{\Omega_{t}}
,
where
T_{\!{p_{\tau}}}{\Omega_{\tau}}
is the tangent space at
{\Omega_{\tau}}
at
{p_{\tau}}
.

G.1.2
Quantification with bases (matrix representation)

Classical notations:
({\vec{a}}_{i})
is a basis in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
, and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
is a basis in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
. Marsden–Hughes duality notations:
({\vec{E}}_{I})
is a basis in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
and
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is a basis in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
.
And the reference to the points
P
and
p
is omitted to lighten the writings (use the full notation of §
G.1.1
if in doubt).

Let
[G]:=[({\vec{a}}_{i},{\vec{a}}_{j})_{G}]
,
[g]:=[({\vec{b}}_{i},{\vec{b}}_{j})_{g}]
,

[F]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}=[F_{ij}]{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}[F]
,

[F^{T}]_{|{\vec{b}},{\vec{a}}}=[(F^{T})_{ij}]{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}[F^{T}]
.

(
G.1
) gives
[{\vec{U}}]^{T}.[G].[F^{T}.{\vec{w}}]=[F.{\vec{U}}]^{T}.[G].[{\vec{w}}]
,
thus
[{\vec{U}}]^{T}.[G].[F^{T}].[{\vec{w}}]=[{\vec{U}}]^{T}.[F]^{T}.[g].[{\vec{w}}]
, for all
{\vec{U}},{\vec{w}}
, thus

[G].[F^{T}]=[F]^{T}.[g],\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle[F^{T}]=[G]^{-1}.[F]^{T}.[g]$}.

(G.3)

Remark G.3

If
({\vec{a}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
are
(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-orthonormal bases, then
[C]=[F]^{T}.[F]
.
But recall: If you need to work with a coordinate system, then the bases in use are the coordinate system bases which are
not
orthonormal in general, i.e.
[G]^{-1}\neq I
and
[g]^{-1}\neq I
in general.

Exercice G.4

Use classical notation, then Marsden duality notations, to express (
G.3
) with components.

Answer
. Classical notations:

\displaystyle{{}G_{ij}=G({\vec{a}}_{i},{\vec{a}}_{j}),\hskip 9.24994ptg_{ij}=g({\vec{b}}_{i},{\vec{b}}_{j}),\hskip 9.24994pt\hbox{i.e.}\hskip 9.24994pt[G]_{{\vec{a}}}=[G_{ij}],\hskip 9.24994pt[g]_{|{\vec{b}}}=[g_{ij}],}

and

\displaystyle{{}F.{\vec{a}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}F_{ij}{\vec{b}}_{i},\hskip 9.24994ptF^{T}.{\vec{b}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}(F^{T})_{ij}{\vec{a}}_{i},\hskip 9.24994pt\hbox{i.e.}\hskip 9.24994pt[F]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}=[F_{ij}],\hskip 9.24994pt[F^{T}]_{|{\vec{b}},{\vec{a}}}=[(F^{T})_{ij}].}

(G.4)

Then
(F^{T}.{\vec{b}}_{j},{\vec{a}}_{i})_{G}\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqFT00})}({\vec{b}}_{j},F.{\vec{a}}_{i})_{g}
gives

({\sum_{k=1}^{n}}(F^{T})_{kj}{\vec{a}}_{k},{\vec{a}}_{i})_{G}=({\vec{b}}_{j},{\sum_{k=1}^{n}}F_{ki}{\vec{b}}_{k})_{g}
, thus

{\sum_{k=1}^{n}}(F^{T})_{kj}({\vec{a}}_{k},{\vec{a}}_{i})_{G}={\sum_{k=1}^{n}}F_{ki}({\vec{b}}_{j},{\vec{b}}_{k})_{g}
with
F_{ki}=([F]^{T})_{ik}
, thus

{\sum_{k=1}^{n}}G_{ik}(F^{T})_{kj}={\sum_{k=1}^{n}}([F]^{T})_{ik}g_{kj},\hskip 9.24994pt\hbox{i.e.}\hskip 9.24994pt(F^{T})_{ij}=\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{n}([G]^{-1})_{ik}F_{\ell k}g_{\ell j},

(G.5)

for all
i,j
, thus (
G.3
).

Marsden notations:

G_{IJ}=G({\vec{E}}_{I},{\vec{E}}_{j})
,
g_{ij}=g({\vec{e}}_{i},{\vec{e}}_{j})
,
F.{\vec{E}}_{J}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{F^{i}_{\;J}}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,
F^{T}.{\vec{e}}_{j}={\sum_{I=1}^{n}}(F^{T})^{I}{}_{j}{\vec{E}}_{I}
, thus

\displaystyle\sum_{K=1}^{n}G_{IK}(F^{T})^{K}_{\;\;\;j}={\sum_{k=1}^{n}}F^{k}_{\;\;I}g_{kj},\hskip 9.24994pt\hbox{i.e.}\hskip 9.24994pt(F^{T})^{I}{}_{j}=\sum_{K,k=1}^{n}G^{IK}F^{k}_{\;K}g_{kj}\hskip 9.24994pt\hbox{where}\hskip 9.24994pt[G^{IJ}]:=[G_{IJ}]^{-1}.

G.1.3
Remark:
F^{*}

(For mathematicians:
F^{*}
doesn’t seem to be very useful in mechanics, apart from making simple things difficult, and playing games with components and duality notations…).

Definition G.5

The adjoint of the linear map
F\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})
(acting on vectors) is the linear map

F^{*}\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n*}_{t}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n*}_{t_{0}}})
(acting on functions) canonically defined by,
for all
m\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n*}_{t}}
,

F^{*}(m):=m\circ F,\quad\hbox{written}\quad F^{*}.m=m.F\;\;(\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n*}_{t_{0}}}).

(G.6)

So, for all
(m,{\vec{W}})\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n*}_{t}}\times{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
,

(F^{*}.m).{\vec{W}}=m.F.{\vec{W}}\;\;(\in\mathbb{R}).

(G.7)

Quantification
(matrix representation):

(\pi_{ai})
and
(\pi_{bi})
are the covariant dual bases of
({\vec{a}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
.
Let
(F^{*})_{ij}
be the components of
F^{*}
relative to these dual bases:

F^{*}.\pi_{bj}={\sum_{I=1}^{n}}(F^{*})_{ij}\pi_{ai},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad[F^{*}]_{|\pi_{b},\pi_{a}}=[(F^{*})_{ij}].

(G.8)

(
G.7
) gives
(F^{*}.\pi_{bj}).{\vec{a}}_{i}=\pi_{bj}.F.{\vec{a}}_{i}
, thus

\forall i,j,\;\framebox{$\displaystyle(F^{*})_{ij}=F_{ji}$},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad[F^{*}]_{|\pi_{b},\pi_{a}}=([F]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}})^{T},\quad\hbox{in short}\quad[F^{*}]=[F]^{T}.

(G.9)

Marsden duality notations:
F^{*}.e^{j}={\sum_{I=1}^{n}}(F^{*})_{I}{}^{j}E^{I}
gives
(F^{*})_{I}{}^{j}=F^{j}{}_{I}
for all
I,j
.

Interpretation of
F^{*}
.
As usual in classical mechanics, we use Euclidean dot products, here
(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
. Then we use
the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}
-Riesz representation vector
{\vec{R}}_{G}(F^{*}.m)\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
of
F^{*}.m\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n*}_{t_{0}}}
, and the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Riesz representation vector
{\vec{R}}_{g}(m)\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
of
m\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n*}_{t}}
, so, for all
m\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n*}_{t}}
and
{\vec{W}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
,

(F^{*}.m).{\vec{W}}={\vec{R}}_{G}(F^{*}.m)\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!G$}}}\,{\vec{W}},\quad\hbox{and}\quad m.(F.{\vec{W}})={\vec{R}}_{g}(m)\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,F.{\vec{W}}=(F^{T}.{\vec{R}}_{g}(m))\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!G$}}}\,{\vec{W}}.

(G.10)

Thus
(
G.7
) gives

{\vec{R}}_{G}(F^{*}.m)=F^{T}.{\vec{R}}_{g}(m)
, thus

{\vec{R}}_{G}.F^{*}=F^{T}.{\vec{R}}_{g},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad F^{*}={\vec{R}}_{G}{}^{-1}.F^{T}.{\vec{R}}_{g}.

(G.11)

Remark G.6

The definition of
F^{*}
is intrinsic to
F
(objective), while the definition of
F^{T}
is
not
intrinsic to
F
(
not
objective) since it needs inner dot products (observer choices) to be defined.

G.2
Cauchy–Green deformation tensor
C

G.2.1
Definition of
C

Consider vectors
{\vec{W}}_{i}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
at
P
,
i=1,2
,
and their push forwards
{\vec{w}}_{i}
toward
p=\Phi(P)
, i.e.

{\vec{w}}_{i}=F.{\vec{W}}_{i},

(G.12)

short notation for
{\vec{w}}_{i}(p)=F(P).{\vec{W}}_{i}(P)
.
With the chosen inner dot products
(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
, we get

({{\vec{w}}_{1}}(p),{{\vec{w}}_{2}}(p))_{g}=(F(P).{{\vec{W}}_{1}}(P),F(P).{{\vec{W}}_{2}}(P))_{g}\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqFT0})}}(F^{T}_{Gg}(p).F(P).{{\vec{W}}_{1}}(P),{{\vec{W}}_{2}}(P))_{G}
when
p=\Phi(P)
, written in short:

({{\vec{w}}_{1}},{{\vec{w}}_{2}})_{g}=(F.{{\vec{W}}_{1}},F.{{\vec{W}}_{2}})_{g}=(\underbrace{F^{T}.F}_{C}.{{\vec{W}}_{1}},{{\vec{W}}_{2}})_{G},

(G.13)

Definition G.7

The (right) Cauchy–Green deformation tensor at
P\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
relative to
(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
, is the endomorphism

{C_{Gg}}(P)\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}})
defined by

{C_{Gg}}(P):=F^{T}_{Gg}(p)\circ F(P),\quad\hbox{in short}\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle C:=F^{T}.F$}.

(G.14)

So

C=F^{T}\circ F:{\vec{W}}\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{F}F({\vec{W}})\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{F^{T}}F^{T}(F({\vec{W}}))=C({\vec{W}}),

(G.15)

with
F
and
F^{T}
linear, thus
C
is linear and
C({\vec{W}})
is written
C.{\vec{W}}=F^{T}.F.{\vec{W}}
.
And (
G.13
) tells that
C
is characterized by, for all
{{\vec{W}}_{1}},{{\vec{W}}_{2}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
,

{{\vec{w}}_{1}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,{{\vec{w}}_{2}}=\framebox{$\displaystyle(C.{{\vec{W}}_{1}})\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!G$}}}\,{{\vec{W}}_{2}}=(F.{{\vec{W}}_{1}})\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,(F.{{\vec{W}}_{2}})$}.

(G.16)

Moreover,
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
being symmetric (inner dot product),
C
is a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}
-symmetric endomorphism in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
,
i.e., for all
{{\vec{W}}_{1}},{{\vec{W}}_{2}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
,

(C.{{\vec{W}}_{1}},{{\vec{W}}_{2}})_{G}=({{\vec{W}}_{1}},C.{{\vec{W}}_{2}})_{G},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad(C.{{\vec{W}}_{1}})\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!G$}}}\,{{\vec{W}}_{2}}={{\vec{W}}_{1}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!G$}}}\,(C.{{\vec{W}}_{2}}),

(G.17)

since
(F^{T}.F.{{\vec{W}}_{1}},{{\vec{W}}_{2}})_{G}=(F.{{\vec{W}}_{1}},F.{{\vec{W}}_{2}})_{g}=({{\vec{W}}_{1}},F^{T}.F.{{\vec{W}}_{2}})_{G}
.

G.2.2
Quantification

(
G.14
) gives
[C]=[F^{T}].[F]
, with
[F^{T}]\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqr2})}[G]^{-1}.[F]^{T}.[g]
, thus

\framebox{$\displaystyle[C]=[G]^{-1}.[F]^{T}.[g].[F]$},

(G.18)

short notation for

[C_{Gg}]_{|{\vec{a}}}=[G]_{|{\vec{a}}}^{-1}.([F]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}})^{T}.[g]_{|{\vec{b}}}.[F]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}
.

Exercice G.8

Use classical notation, then duality notations, to express (
G.18
) with components.

Answer
. Classical notations:

F.{\vec{a}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}F_{ij}{\vec{b}}_{i}\hskip 9.24994pt\hbox{and}\hskip 9.24994ptC.{\vec{a}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}C_{ij}{\vec{a}}_{i},\hskip 9.24994pt\hbox{i.e.}\hskip 9.24994pt[F]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}=[F_{ij}]\hskip 9.24994pt\hbox{and}\hskip 9.24994pt[C]_{|{\vec{a}}}=[C_{ij}].

(G.19)

(
G.16
)-(
G.17
) give

({\vec{a}}_{i},C.{\vec{a}}_{j})_{G}=(F.{\vec{a}}_{i},F.{\vec{a}}_{j})_{g}
,
so

({\vec{a}}_{i},\sum_{k}C_{kj}{\vec{a}}_{k})_{G}=(\sum_{k}F_{ki}{\vec{b}}_{k},\sum_{\ell}F_{\ell j}{\vec{b}}_{\ell})_{g}
,
thus

\sum_{k}C_{kj}({\vec{a}}_{i},{\vec{a}}_{k})_{G}=\sum_{k\ell}F_{ki}({\vec{b}}_{k},{\vec{b}}_{\ell})_{g}F_{\ell j}
,
i.e.

{\sum_{k=1}^{n}}G_{ik}C_{kj}={\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{n}}F_{ki}\,g_{k\ell}F_{\ell j}={\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{n}}([F]^{T})_{ik}\,g_{k\ell}F_{\ell j},\hskip 9.24994pt\hbox{so}\hskip 9.24994pt\framebox{$\displaystyle[G].[C]=[F]^{T}.[g].[F]$},

(G.20)

so

C_{ij}=\sum_{k,\ell,m=1}^{n}([G]^{-1})_{im}F_{km}\,g_{k\ell}F_{\ell j}=\sum_{k,\ell,m=1}^{n}([G]^{-1})_{im}([F]^{T})_{mk}\,g_{k\ell}F_{\ell j}
. Duality notations:

\displaystyle{{}F.{\vec{E}}_{J}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{F^{i}_{\;J}}{\vec{e}}_{i}\hskip 9.24994pt\hbox{and}\hskip 9.24994ptC.{\vec{E}}_{J}={\sum_{I=1}^{n}}{C^{I}{}_{\!\!J}}{\vec{E}}_{I},\hskip 9.24994pt\hbox{i.e.}\hskip 9.24994pt[F]_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{e}}}=[{F^{i}_{\;J}}]\hskip 9.24994pt\hbox{and}\hskip 9.24994pt[C]_{|{\vec{E}}}=[{C^{I}{}_{\!\!J}}],\;\hbox{ and}}

\displaystyle{{}{\sum_{K=1}^{n}}G_{IK}C^{K}{}_{\!\!J}=\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{n}F^{k}{}_{\!I}\,g_{k\ell}F^{\ell}{}_{\!\!J},\hskip 9.24994pt\hbox{and}\hskip 9.24994pt{C^{I}{}_{\!\!J}}=\sum_{k,\ell,M=1}^{n}G^{IM}F^{k}{}_{\!\!M}\,g_{k\ell}F^{\ell}{}_{\!\!J}\hskip 9.24994pt\hbox{when}\hskip 9.24994pt[G^{IJ}]:=[G_{IJ}]^{-1}.}

(G.21)

Exercice G.9

(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}
is a Euclidean dot product in foot,

(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
is a Euclidean dot product in metre, so

(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}=\mu^{2}(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}
with
\mu\simeq 0.3048
; And
({\vec{a}}_{i})=({\vec{b}}_{i})
is a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}
-orthonormal basis. Prove

[C]=\mu^{2}[F]^{T}.[F].

(G.22)

Answer
.
[C]_{|{\vec{a}}}\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqr30})}}[G]_{|{\vec{a}}}^{-1}.[F]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{a}}}^{T}.[g]_{|{\vec{a}}}.[F]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{a}}}
gives

[C]_{|{\vec{a}}}=I.[F]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{a}}}^{T}.\mu^{2}I.[F]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{a}}}
. Shorten notation = (
G.22
).

G.3
Time Taylor expansion of
C

Here we use a unique inner dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}=(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
at all time
(to compare results in the vicinity of
{t_{0}}
). Moreover we use an orthonormal basis (to lighten the notations),
thus, in short,
[C]=[F]^{T}.[F]
.

P
is fixed,

{C^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}C(t)
, and
[C(t)]=[F(t)]^{T}.[F(t)]
(since
[G]=[g]=I
here),
and
{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{V}}(t)
and
{{\vec{A}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{A}}(t)
are the Lagrangian velocities and accelerations,
and
{\vec{v}}(t,p)
and
{\vec{\gamma}}(t,p)
are the Eulerian velocities and accelerations at
t
at
p={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(t,P)
.

With Lagrangian variables (used to define
C
):

F(t{+}h)=F(t)+h\,d{\vec{V}}(t)+{h^{2}\over 2}\,d{\vec{A}}(t)+o(h^{2})
gives

\displaystyle{[C(t{+}h)]=}

\displaystyle{{}[F(t{+}h)]^{T}.[F(t{+}h)]}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}[F^{T}+h\,d{\vec{V}}^{T}+{h^{2}\over 2}\,d{\vec{A}}^{T}+o(h^{2})](t)[F+h\,d{\vec{V}}+{h^{2}\over 2}\,d{\vec{A}}+o(h^{2})](t)}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}[C(t)+h\,\underbrace{([F^{T}].[d{\vec{V}}]+[d{\vec{V}}]^{T}.[F])(t)}_{=[({C^{t_{0}}_{P}})^{\prime}(t)]{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}[C^{\prime}(t)]}+{h^{2}\over 2}\,\underbrace{([F]^{T}.[d{\vec{A}}]+2[d{\vec{V}}]^{T}.[d{\vec{V}}]+[d{\vec{A}}]^{T}.[F])(t)}_{=[({C^{t_{0}}_{P}})^{\prime\prime}(t)]{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}[C^{\prime\prime}(t)]})(t)+o(h^{2}).}

(G.23)

(As usual with Lagrangian variables, we have three times involved:
{t_{0}}
,
t
and
t{+}h)
.) In particular

[{C^{t_{0}}_{P}}({t_{0}}{+}h)]=I+([d{\vec{V}}]+[d{\vec{V}}]^{T})({t_{0}})+{h^{2}\over 2}\,([d{\vec{A}}]+2[d{\vec{V}}]^{T}.[d{\vec{V}}]+[d{\vec{A}}]^{T})({t_{0}})+o(h^{2}).

(G.24)

Abusively written
{C^{t_{0}}_{P}}({t_{0}}{+}h)=I+(d{\vec{V}}+d{\vec{V}}^{T})({t_{0}})+{h^{2}\over 2}\,(d{\vec{A}}+2d{\vec{V}}^{T}.d{\vec{V}}+d{\vec{A}}^{T})({t_{0}})+o(h^{2})
, but don’t forget it is a matrix meaning.

With Eulerian variables: With

p(t)={\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,P)
, we have

d{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}}(t,P)=d{\vec{v}}(t,p(t)).F(t)
and
d{{\vec{A}}^{t_{0}}}(t,P)=d{\vec{\gamma}}(t,p(t)).F(t)
, thus
writing
d{\vec{v}}:=d{\vec{v}}(t,p(t))
and
d{\vec{\gamma}}:=d{\vec{\gamma}}(t,p(t))
(for short),

\displaystyle{{C^{t_{0}}_{P}}(t{+}h)=}

\displaystyle{{}{C^{t_{0}}_{P}}(t)+h\,(F^{T}(t).(d{\vec{v}}+d{\vec{v}}^{T})(t,p(t)).F(t))}

\displaystyle{{}\quad+{h^{2}\over 2}\,(F^{T}(t).(d{\vec{\gamma}}+2d{\vec{v}}^{T}.d{\vec{v}}+d{\vec{\gamma}}^{T})(t,p(t)).F(t))+o(h^{2}).}

(G.25)

abusive notation of
[{C^{t_{0}}_{P}}(t{+}h)]=...
(matrices relative to a basis).

Remark G.10

F^{\prime\prime}=d{\vec{A}}
is easy to interpret, but

C^{\prime\prime}=F^{T}.d{\vec{A}}+2d{\vec{V}}^{T}.d{\vec{V}}+d{\vec{A}}^{T}.F=(F^{T}.d{\vec{A}}+d{\vec{V}}^{T}.d{\vec{V}})+(F^{T}.d{\vec{A}}+d{\vec{V}}^{T}.d{\vec{V}})^{T}

is not that easy to interpret (and in not linear in
{\vec{V}}
).

We already had a problem with the composition of flows: The formula

F^{t_{0}}_{t_{2}}=F^{t_{1}}_{t_{2}}.F^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}}
is simple (determinism), but the formula

C^{t_{0}}_{t_{2}}=(F^{t_{0}}_{t_{2}})^{T}.F^{t_{0}}_{t_{2}}=(F^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}})^{T}.(F^{t_{1}}_{t_{2}})^{T}.F^{t_{1}}_{t_{2}}.F^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}}=(F^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}})^{T}.C^{t_{1}}_{t_{2}}.F^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}}
is “not that simple” (
\neq C^{t_{1}}_{t_{2}}.C^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}}
).
(Indeed, to consider
C
instead of
F
amounts to consider the “motion squared”, cf.
(C.{\vec{W}},{\vec{W}})_{g}=||F.{\vec{W}}||_{g}^{2}
.)

Since
C^{\prime}({t_{0}})=d{\vec{V}}({t_{0}})+d{\vec{V}}({t_{0}})^{T}
this may have little consequences for linear approximation near
{t_{0}}
, but ultimately not small consequences for second-order approximations (and large deformations) if
C^{\prime\prime}
is used to make constitutive laws. The consideration of Lie derivatives may be an interesting alternative.

G.4
Remark:
C^{\flat}

For mathematicians: May produce errors, misuses, covariance-contravariance confusion, see next §
G.4.2
.
For the general
♭
notation see §
A.10.4
.

G.4.1
Definition of
C^{\flat}
…

Definition G.11

At
P\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
, the bilinear form
C^{\flat}_{Gg}(P){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}C^{\flat}\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}},{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};\mathbb{R})
associated with the linear map
C_{Gg}(P){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}C\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}})
is defined by, for all
{{\vec{W}}_{1}},{{\vec{W}}_{2}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
vectors at
P
,

C^{\flat}({{\vec{W}}_{1}},{{\vec{W}}_{2}}):=({{\vec{W}}_{1}},C.{{\vec{W}}_{2}})_{G}\quad(=(F.{{\vec{W}}_{1}},F.{{\vec{W}}_{2}})_{g}).

(G.26)

Then
C^{\flat}
is a bilinear symmetric form (trivial) and is a metric in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
when
{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}F
is a diffeomorphism (usual hypothesis),
but not a Euclidean one (it is iff
C=I
i.e. for rigid body motions).

Quantification:
(
G.26
) gives
[{{\vec{W}}_{2}}]^{T}.[C^{\flat}].[{{\vec{W}}_{1}}]=[{{\vec{W}}_{2}}]^{T}.[G].[C].[{{\vec{W}}_{1}}]
for all
{{\vec{W}}_{1}},{{\vec{W}}_{2}}
since
C^{\flat}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}
are symmetric, thus

[C^{\flat}]=[G].[C]\quad(=[F]^{T}.[g].[F]).

(G.27)

Exercice G.12

Use duality notations to express (
G.27
) with components,
and explain the flat
♭
notation.

Answer
. (
G.26
) gives
C^{\flat}({\vec{E}}_{J},{\vec{E}}_{I}):=(C.{\vec{E}}_{J},{\vec{E}}_{I})_{G}
.
Thus with
C^{\flat}({\vec{E}}_{I},{\vec{E}}_{J})=C_{IJ}
and
C.{\vec{E}}_{J}=\sum_{I}{C^{I}{}_{\!\!J}}{\vec{E}}_{I}

we get

C_{JI}=\sum_{K}C^{K}{}_{\!J}({\vec{E}}_{K},{\vec{E}}_{I})_{G}=\sum_{K}C^{K}{}_{\!J}G_{KI}
;
And
C^{\flat}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}
are symmetric, thus

C_{IJ}=\sum_{K}G_{IK}C^{K}{}_{\!J},\hskip 9.24994pt\hbox{i.e.}\hskip 9.24994pt[C^{\flat}]_{|{\vec{E}}}=[G]_{|{\vec{E}}}.[C]_{|{\vec{E}}}.

(G.28)

The flat notation
C^{\flat}
is due to:
The top index
I
in
{C^{I}{}_{\!\!J}}
has been transformed into a bottom index in
C_{IJ}
in
C^{\flat}
, which characterizes a change of variance because of the use of an inner dot product.

And (
G.27
) also gives

C_{IJ}=(F.{\vec{E}}_{I},F.{\vec{E}}_{J})_{g}=\sum_{k\ell}F^{k}{}_{I}F^{\ell}{}_{J}({\vec{b}}_{k},{\vec{b}}_{\ell})_{g}
, thus

C_{IJ}=\sum_{k\ell}F^{k}{}_{\!I}g_{k\ell}F^{\ell}{}_{\!J}=\sum_{k\ell}(F^{T})^{I}{}_{\!k}g_{k\ell}F^{\ell}{}_{\!J},\hskip 9.24994pt\hbox{i.e.}\hskip 9.24994pt[C^{\flat}]_{|{\vec{E}}}=([F]_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{e}}})^{T}.[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[F]_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{e}}}.

(G.29)

G.4.2
… and remarks about
C^{\flat}
… and Jaumann

C^{\flat}
can also be defined only with
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
by, for all
{{\vec{W}}_{1}},{{\vec{W}}_{2}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
,

C^{\flat}_{g}({{\vec{W}}_{1}},{{\vec{W}}_{2}}):=(F.{{\vec{W}}_{1}},F.{{\vec{W}}_{2}})_{g},

(G.30)

i.e.,
C^{\flat}_{g}:=g^{*}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}C^{\flat}
. So we can also say that
C^{\flat}_{g}
is the pull-back of the metric
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
by
\Phi
, see (
8.9
).

\bullet
However
C^{\flat}=C^{\flat}_{g}
is useless in itself:
C^{\flat}
is
not
a Euclidean dot product (it is a metric defined at each
P
by
C^{\flat}_{g}(P)({{\vec{W}}_{1}},{{\vec{W}}_{2}}):=(F(P).{{\vec{W}}_{1}},F(P).{{\vec{W}}_{2}})_{g}
for all
{{\vec{W}}_{1}},{{\vec{W}}_{2}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
vectors at
P
).
In fact,
C^{\flat}
is only useful to characterize a deformation if the value
C^{\flat}({{\vec{W}}_{1}},{{\vec{W}}_{2}})
can be compared with the initial value
({{\vec{W}}_{1}},{{\vec{W}}_{2}})_{G}
, i.e. if a Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}
was introduced in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
: This is why
C^{\flat}
is classically defined from
C
, cf. (
G.26
).

\bullet
You may want to use the infinitesimal strain tensor
{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}={F+F^{T}\over 2}-I
, or
the Green–Lagrange deformation tensor
E={1\over 2}(C-I)
, obtained from
F^{T}:=F_{Gg}^{T}
(essential).

\bullet
There is no objective “trace” for a
{0\choose 2}
tensor like
C^{\flat}
,
while
{\rm Tr}(C)
is objective since
C
is an endomorphism (
\simeq
a
{1\choose 1}
tensor).

\bullet
The Lie derivatives of a second order tensor depends on the type of the tensor, and the Lie derivative of the
{1\choose 1}
tensor like
C
gives the Jaumann derivative, which is usually preferred
to the Lie derivative of the
{0\choose 2}
tensor like
C^{\flat}
which is the lower convected Lie derivative, see remark
G.13
.

So the introduction and use of
C^{\flat}
in mechanics mostly complicate things unnecessarily, and interferes with basic understandings like the distinction between covariance and contravariance.

Remark G.13

Interpretation issue (with Jaumann).

2{\cal D}=d{\vec{v}}+d{\vec{v}}^{T}
gives
2{D{\cal D}\over Dt}={D(d{\vec{v}})\over Dt}+{D(d{\vec{v}})^{T}\over Dt}=d{\vec{\gamma}}+d{\vec{\gamma}}^{T}-d{\vec{v}}.d{\vec{v}}-d{\vec{v}}^{T}.d{\vec{v}}^{T}
, thus, with (
G.23
) and keeping in mind the matrix meaning,

\displaystyle{C^{\prime\prime}(t)=}

\displaystyle{{}F(t)^{T}.(2{D{\cal D}\over Dt}+d{\vec{v}}.d{\vec{v}}+d{\vec{v}}^{T}.d{\vec{v}}^{T}+2d{\vec{v}}^{T}.d{\vec{v}})(t,p(t)).F(t)}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}2F(t)^{T}.({D{\cal D}\over Dt}+{\cal D}.d{\vec{v}}+d{\vec{v}}^{T}.{\cal D})(t,p(t)).F(t).}

(G.31)

The
{D{\cal D}\over Dt}+{\cal D}.d{\vec{v}}+d{\vec{v}}^{T}.{\cal D}
term looks like a lower-convected Lie derivative,
but with
d{\vec{v}}^{T}
instead of
d{\vec{v}}^{*}
, cf. (
9.58
);
So you may find (
G.31
) written as
C^{\prime\prime}=2F^{T}.{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\cal D}.F
.
But you get disappointing results when using the the lower convected Lie derivative (Jaumann is usually preferred).
In fact, it is
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\cal D}^{\flat}
(lower convected Lie derivative) that should be used,
where
{\cal D}_{g}^{\flat}:={d{\vec{v}}^{\flat}_{g}+(d{\vec{v}}^{\flat}_{g})^{T}\over 2}
, to get
(C^{\flat})^{\prime\prime}=2F^{T}.{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\cal D}_{g}^{\flat}.F
.

G.5
Stretch ratio and deformed angle

Here
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}=(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}
, i.e. at
{t_{0}}
and
t
we use the same Euclidean dot product, to be able to compare the lengths relative to the same unit of measurement.
(If
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}\neq(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}
then use
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}=\mu^{2}(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}
.)

G.5.1
Stretch ratio

The stretch ratio at
P\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
between
{t_{0}}
and
t
for a
{\vec{W}}_{P}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
is defined by

\lambda({\vec{W}}_{P}):={||{\vec{w}}_{p}||_{G}\over||{\vec{W}}_{P}||_{G}}={||F_{P}.{\vec{W}}_{P}||_{G}\over||{\vec{W}}_{P}||_{G}}\quad(=||F_{P}.({{\vec{W}}_{P}\over||{\vec{W}}_{P}||_{G}})||_{G})

(G.32)

where
{\vec{w}}_{p}=F_{P}.{\vec{W}}_{P}
is the deformed vector by the motion at
p=\Phi(P)
.
I.e., in short

\forall{\vec{W}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}\;\;\hbox{s{.}t{.}}\;\;||{\vec{W}}||=1,\quad\lambda({\vec{W}}):=||F.{\vec{W}}||.

(G.33)

(You may find:

\lambda(d{\vec{X}})=||F.d{\vec{X}}||
with
d{\vec{X}}
a unit vector(!); This notation should be avoided, see §
4.3
.)

G.5.2
Deformed angle

Recall: The angle
\theta_{t_{0}}=\widehat{({{\vec{W}}_{1}},{{\vec{W}}_{2}})}

formed by two vectors
{{\vec{W}}_{1}}
and
{{\vec{W}}_{2}}
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t_{0}}}}{-}\{\vec{0}\}
at
P\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
is given by

\cos(\theta_{t_{0}})={{{\vec{W}}_{1}}\over||{{\vec{W}}_{1}}||_{G}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{{{\vec{W}}_{2}}\over||{{\vec{W}}_{2}}||_{G}}

(
=({{{\vec{W}}_{1}}\over||{{\vec{W}}_{1}}||_{G}},{{{\vec{W}}_{2}}\over||{{\vec{W}}_{2}}||_{G}})_{G}
).

With the deformed vectors
{\vec{w}}_{i}=F.{\vec{W}}_{i}
at
p={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
, the deformed angle is
\theta_{t}
defined by

\cos(\theta_{t}):=\widehat{({{\vec{w}}_{1}},{{\vec{w}}_{2}})}={{{\vec{w}}_{1}}\over||{{\vec{w}}_{1}}||}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{{{\vec{w}}_{2}}\over||{{\vec{w}}_{2}}||}={F.{{\vec{W}}_{1}}\over||F.{{\vec{W}}_{1}}||}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{F.{{\vec{W}}_{2}}\over||F.{{\vec{W}}_{2}}||}\quad(={(C.{{\vec{W}}_{1}})\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{{\vec{W}}_{2}}\over||{{\vec{w}}_{1}}||\,||{{\vec{w}}_{2}}||}).

(G.34)

G.6
Decompositions of
C

G.6.1
Spherical and deviatoric tensors

Definition G.14

The deformation spheric tensor is

C_{sph}={1\over n}{\rm Tr}(C)\;I,

(G.35)

with
{\rm Tr}(C)=
the trace of the endomorphism
C
(there is no “trace” for the
{0\choose 2}
tensor
C^{\flat}
).

Definition G.15

The deviatoric tensor is

C_{dev}=C-C_{sph}.

(G.36)

(So
{\rm Tr}(C_{dev})=0
, and
C=C_{sph}+C_{dev}
.)

G.6.2
Rigid motion

The deformation is rigid iff, for all
{t_{0}},t
,

({F^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{T}.{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}=I,\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad{C^{t_{0}}_{t}}=I,\quad\hbox{written}\quad C=I=F^{T}.F.

(G.37)

Thus, after a rigid body motion, lengths and angles are left unchanged.

G.6.3
Diagonalization of
C

Proposition G.16

C=F^{T}.F
being symmetric positive,

C
is diagonalizable, its eigenvalues are positive, and
{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t_{0}}}}
has an orthonormal basis made of eigenvectors of
C
.

Proof.
(C(P).{{\vec{W}}_{1}},{{\vec{W}}_{2}})_{G}=(F(P).{{\vec{W}}_{1}},F(P).{{\vec{W}}_{2}})_{g}=({{\vec{W}}_{1}},C(P).{{\vec{W}}_{2}})_{G}
, thus
C
is
(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}
-symmetric.

(C.{{\vec{W}}_{1}},{{\vec{W}}_{1}})_{G}=(F.{{\vec{W}}_{1}},F.{{\vec{W}}_{1}})_{g}=||F.{{\vec{W}}_{1}}||_{g}^{2}>0
when
{{\vec{W}}_{1}}\neq\vec{0}
,
since
F
invertible (
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
is supposed to be a diffeomorphism).
Thus
C
est
(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}
-symmetric definite positive real endomorphism.

Definition G.17

Let
\lambda_{i}
be the eigenvalues of
C
.
Then the
\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}
are called the principal stretches.
And the associated eigenvectors give the principal directions.

G.6.4
Mohr circle

This § deals with general properties of
3*3
symmetric positive endomorphism, like
{C^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
.

Consider
{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}
with a Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}
and a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
-orthonormal basis
({\vec{a}}_{i})
.

Let
{\cal M}:{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}
be a symmetric positive endomorphism.
Thus
{\cal M}
is diagonalizable in a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
-orthonormal basis
({\vec{e}}_{1},{\vec{e}}_{2},{\vec{e}}_{3})
, that is,

\exists\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\exists{\vec{e}}_{1},{\vec{e}}_{2},{\vec{e}}_{3}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}
s.t.

{\cal M}.{\vec{e}}_{i}=\lambda_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}\quad\hbox{and}\quad({\vec{e}}_{i},{\vec{e}}_{j})_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}=\delta_{ij},\quad\hbox{so}\quad[{\cal M}]_{|{\vec{e}}}={\rm diag}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})=\pmatrix{\lambda_{1}&0&0\cr 0&\lambda_{2}&0\cr 0&0&\lambda_{3}}.

(G.38)

And the orthonormal basis
({\vec{e}}_{1},{\vec{e}}_{2},{\vec{e}}_{3})
is ordered s.t.
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\lambda_{3}
(
>0
).

Let
S
be the unit sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, that is the set
\{(x,y,z):x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2}=1\}
.
Its image
{\cal M}(S)
by
{\cal M}
is the ellipsoid

\{(x,y,z):{x^{2}\over\lambda_{1}^{2}}+{y^{2}\over\lambda_{2}^{2}}+{z^{2}\over\lambda_{3}^{2}}=1\}
.
Then consider
{\vec{n}}=\sum_{i}n_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
s.t.
||{\vec{n}}||_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}=1
:

[{\vec{n}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{n_{1}\cr n_{2}\cr n_{3}}\quad\hbox{with}\quad n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}+n_{3}^{2}=1.

(G.39)

Thus its image
{\vec{A}}={\cal M}.{\vec{n}}\in{\cal M}(S)
satisfies

{\vec{A}}={\cal M}.{\vec{n}},\quad[{\vec{A}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{\lambda_{1}n_{1}\cr\lambda_{2}n_{2}\cr\lambda_{3}n_{3}\cr}.

(G.40)

Then define

A_{n}=({\vec{A}},{\vec{n}})_{\mathbb{R}^{3}},\quad{\vec{A}}_{\perp}={\vec{A}}-A_{n}{\vec{n}},\quad A_{\perp}:=||{\vec{A}}_{\perp}||.

(G.41)

So
{\vec{A}}=A_{n}{\vec{n}}+{\vec{A}}_{\perp}\;\in\;{\rm Vect}\{{\vec{n}}\}\otimes{\rm Vect}\{{\vec{n}}\}^{\perp}
.
(Remark:
{\vec{A}}_{\perp}
is not orthonormal to the ellipsoid
{\cal M}(S)
, but is orthonormal to the initial sphere
S
.)

Mohr Circle purpose:
To find a relation:

A_{\perp}=f(A_{n}),

(G.42)

relation between “the normal force
A_{n}
” (to the initial sphere) and the “tangent force
A_{\perp}
” (to the initial sphere).

(
G.39
), (
G.40
) and
A_{n}=({\cal M}.{\vec{n}},{\vec{n}})_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
give

\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}+n_{3}^{2}=1,\cr&\lambda_{1}n_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}n_{2}^{2}+\lambda_{3}n_{3}^{2}=A_{n}\cr&\lambda_{1}^{2}n_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2}n_{2}^{2}+\lambda_{3}^{2}n_{3}^{2}=||{\vec{A}}||^{2}=A_{n}^{2}+A_{\perp}^{2}.\crcr}}\,\right.

(G.43)

This is linear system with the unknowns
n_{1}^{2},n_{2}^{2},n_{3}^{2}
. The solution is

\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&n_{1}^{2}={A_{\perp}^{2}+(A_{n}-\lambda_{2})(A_{n}-\lambda_{3})\over(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{3})},\cr&n_{2}^{2}={A_{\perp}^{2}+(A_{n}-\lambda_{3})(A_{n}-\lambda_{1})\over(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3})(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1})},\cr&n_{3}^{2}={A_{\perp}^{2}+(A_{n}-\lambda_{1})(A_{n}-\lambda_{2})\over(\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{1})(\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{2})}.\cr}}\,\right.

(G.44)

The
n_{i}^{2}
being non negative, and with
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}>\lambda_{3}\geq 0
, we get

\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&A_{\perp}^{2}+(A_{n}-\lambda_{2})(A_{n}-\lambda_{3})\geq 0,\cr&A_{\perp}^{2}+(A_{n}-\lambda_{3})(A_{n}-\lambda_{1})\leq 0,\cr&A_{\perp}^{2}+(A_{n}-\lambda_{1})(A_{n}-\lambda_{2})\geq 0.\cr}}\,\right.

(G.45)

Then let
x=A_{n}
and
y=A_{\perp}
, and consider, for some
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, the equation

y^{2}+(x-a)(x-b)=0,\quad\hbox{so}\quad(x-{a{+}b\over 2})^{2}+y^{2}={(a{-}b)^{2}\over 4}.

This is the equation of a circle centered at
({a{+}b\over 2},0)
with radius
{|a{-}b|\over 2}
.

Thus (
G.45
)
2
tells that
A_{n}
and
A_{\perp}
are inside the circle
centered at
({\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{3}\over 2},0)
with radius
{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{3}\over 2}
,
and (
G.45
)
1,3
tell that
A_{n}
and
A_{\perp}
are outside the other circles
(adjacent and included in the first, drawing).

Exercice G.18

What happens if
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=\lambda_{3}>0
?

Answer
. Then

\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}+n_{3}^{2}=1,\cr&n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}+n_{3}^{2}={A_{n}\over\lambda_{1}},\cr&n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}+n_{3}^{2}={A_{n}^{2}+A_{\perp}^{2}\over\lambda_{1}^{2}}.\cr}}\,\right\}

Thus
A_{n}=\lambda_{1}
and
A_{n}^{2}+A_{\perp}^{2}=\lambda_{1}^{2}
, thus
A_{\perp}=0
.
Here
C=\lambda_{1}I
, and we deal with a dilation:
A_{\perp}=0
.

Exercice G.19

What happens if
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}>\lambda_{3}>0
?

Answer
. Then

\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}+n_{3}^{2}=1,\cr&\lambda_{1}(1-n_{3}^{2})+\lambda_{3}n_{3}^{2}=A_{n},\cr&\lambda_{1}^{2}(1-n_{3}^{2})+\lambda_{3}^{2}n_{3}^{2}=A_{n}^{2}+A_{\perp}^{2}.\cr}}\,\right\}

Thus
A_{n}=\lambda_{1}-(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{3})n_{3}^{2}\in[\lambda_{3},\lambda_{1}]
,
and
A_{\perp}=\pm(\lambda_{1}^{2}-(\lambda_{1}^{2}-\lambda_{3}^{2})n_{3}^{2}-A_{n}^{2})^{1\over 2}
,
with
A_{n}^{2}+A_{\perp}^{2}
a point on the circle with radius
\lambda_{1}^{2}(1-n_{3}^{2})+\lambda_{3}^{2}n_{3}^{2}
.

G.7
Green–Lagrange deformation tensor
E

(
G.13
) gives

({{\vec{w}}_{1}},{{\vec{w}}_{2}})_{g}=(F.{{\vec{W}}_{1}},F.{{\vec{W}}_{2}})_{g}=(C.{\vec{W}},{\vec{W}})_{G}
at
p=\Phi(P)
, thus

({{\vec{w}}_{1}},{{\vec{w}}_{2}})_{g}-({{\vec{W}}_{1}},{{\vec{W}}_{2}})_{G}=((C-I).{{\vec{W}}_{1}},{{\vec{W}}_{2}})_{G}.

(G.46)

Definition G.20

The Green–Lagrange tensor (or Green–Saint Venant tensor) at
P
relative to
{t_{0}}
and
t

is the endomorphism
E^{t_{0}}_{t}(P)\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}})
defined by

{E^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P):={{C^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)-I_{t_{0}}\over 2},\quad\hbox{in short}\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle E={C-I\over 2}$}\quad(={F^{T}.F-I\over 2}).

(G.47)

(In particular
E=0
for rigid body motions.)
And

{E^{t_{0}}_{t}}:{\Omega_{t_{0}}}\rightarrow{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}})
is the Green–Lagrange tensor relative to
{t_{0}}
and
t
.

The
{1\over 2}
because
(C.,.)=(F.,F.)
corresponds to the “motion squared”, see the following linearization.

And we get the time Taylor expansion of
{E^{t_{0}}_{P}}(t)={1\over 2}({C^{t_{0}}_{P}}(t)-I_{t_{0}})
with
p(t)={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{P}}(t)

and (
G.25
):

\displaystyle{{E^{t_{0}}_{P}}(t{+}h)=}

\displaystyle{{}{F^{t_{0}}_{P}}(t)^{T}.\bigl(h\,{d{\vec{v}}+d{\vec{v}}^{T}\over 2}+{h^{2}\over 2}\,({d{\vec{\gamma}}+d{\vec{\gamma}}^{T}\over 2}+d{\vec{v}}^{T}.d{\vec{v}})\bigr)(t,p(t)).{F^{t_{0}}_{P}}(t)+o(h^{2})}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}{F^{t_{0}}_{P}}(t)^{T}.\bigl(h\,{\cal D}+h^{2}\,(({D{\cal D}\over Dt}+{\cal D}.d{\vec{v}}+d{\vec{v}}^{T}.{\cal D})(t,p(t))).{F^{t_{0}}_{P}}(t)+o(h^{2}).}

(G.48)

G.8
Small deformations (linearization): The infinitesimal strain tensor
{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}

G.8.1
Landau notations big-
O
and little-
o

Reminder. Let
f,g:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
.

\hbox{$f=O(g)$ near $x_{0}$}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\exists C>0,\;\exists\eta>0,\;\forall x\;s{.}t{.}\ |x-x_{0}|<\eta,\;|f(x)|<C|g(x)|.

(G.49)

and
f
is said to be “comparable with
g
” near
x_{0}
.

If
|g|>0
then the conclusion reads
{|f(x)|\over|g(x)|}<C
;
And
f=O(x^{n})
near
x{=}0
iff
{|f(x)|\over|x^{n}|}<C
near
x{=}0
.

And

\hbox{$f=o(g)$ near $x_{0}$}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\forall\varepsilon>0,\;\exists\eta>0,\;\forall x\;s{.}t{.}\ |x-x_{0}|<\eta,\;|f(x)|<\varepsilon|g(x).

(G.50)

and
f
is said to be “negligible compared with
g
near
x_{0}
”.

If
|g|>0
then the conclusion reads
{|f(x)|\over|g(x)|}\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{x\rightarrow x_{0}}0
.
And
f=o(x^{n})
near
x{=}0
iff
{|f(x)|\over|x^{n}|}\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{x\rightarrow 0}0
.

G.8.2
Definition of the infinitesimal strain tensor
{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}

The motion is supposed to be
C^{2}
.
Along a trajectory, with
{F^{t_{0}}_{P}}({t_{0}})=I
we have, near
{t_{0}}
,

{F^{t_{0}}_{P}}({t_{0}}{+}h)=I+O(h),

(G.51)

thus
{F^{t_{0}}_{P}}({t_{0}}{+}h).{\vec{W}}={\vec{W}}+O(h)
for all
{\vec{W}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
, i.e., near
{t_{0}}
,

||{\vec{w}}-{\vec{W}}||=O(h)\quad\hbox{when}\quad{\vec{w}}={F^{t_{0}}_{P}}({t_{0}}{+}h).{\vec{W}}.

(G.52)

This supposes the use of a unique inner dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}=(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
at all time, and (
G.52
) means

||{\vec{w}}-{\vec{W}}||_{g}=O(h)
near
{t_{0}}
.

Definition G.21

If
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
is an inner dot product, the same at all time, and if
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-orthonormal basis, the same at all time, then the infinitesimal strain tensor at
P
is the matrix defined by

[{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}(P)]_{|{\vec{e}}}={[F(P)]_{|{\vec{e}}}+[F(P)]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}\over 2}-[I],

(G.53)

abusively written in short,

{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}:={F+F^{T}\over 2}-I\quad\hbox{(matrix meaning)}.

(G.54)

(And more precisely, at
P\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
and between
{t_{0}}
and
t
,

[{{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}}(P)]_{|{\vec{e}}}={[{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)]_{|{\vec{e}}}+[{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}\over 2}-[I]
.)

So
{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}.{\vec{W}}={F.{\vec{W}}+F^{T}.{\vec{W}}\over 2}-.{\vec{W}}

means

[{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{W}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}={[F]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{W}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}+[F]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}.[{\vec{W}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}\over 2}-[{\vec{W}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}
.

Remark G.22

{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}
in (
G.54
) can
not
be a tensor (cannot be a function) since

{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P):{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t_{0}}}}\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t}}}

and
{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)^{T}:{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t}}}\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t_{0}}}}
and
I_{t_{0}}:{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t_{0}}}}\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t_{0}}}}
don’t have the same definition domain.

So
{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}
is not a function, is not a tensor: It is a matrix… But is called “the infinitesimal strain tensor”…

Proposition G.23

The Green–Lagrange tensor
E={F^{T}.F-I\over 2}\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}})
satisfies near
{t_{0}}
:

E={\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}+o(t{-}{t_{0}})\quad(={F+F^{T}\over 2}-I+o(t{-}{t_{0}}))\quad\hbox{(matrix meaning)},

(G.55)

which means
[E]=[{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}]+o(t{-}{t_{0}})
.
Thus, “for small deformations” we write
E\simeq{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}
, i.e.
E\simeq{F+F^{T}\over 2}-I
.

Interpretation: (
G.55
) is a linearization of
E
, since we keep the linear part of
the “quadratic”
E={1\over 2}(F^{T}.F-I)
given by
(E.{\vec{W}},{\vec{U}})_{g}={1\over 2}\bigl((F.{\vec{W}},F.{\vec{U}})_{g}-({\vec{W}},{\vec{U}})_{g}\bigr)
for all
{\vec{U}},{\vec{W}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
(“motion squared” cf. the
(F\cdot,F\cdot)_{g}
term).

Proof.
A
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-orthonormal basis being chosen,
[F^{T}]\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqr2})}[F]^{T}
, thus

[C]=[F]^{T}.[F]
, thus

2[E]=[C]-[I]=[F]^{T}.[F]-[I]=([F]^{T}-[I).([F]-[I)+[F]^{T}+[F]-2[I].

(G.56)

Then, near
{t_{0}}
and with
h=t{-}{t_{0}}
, (
G.51
) gives

([F]^{T}-[I]).([F]-I])=O(h)O(h)=O(h^{2})
, thus

2[E]=[F]^{T}+[F]-2[I]+O(h)
,
thus (
G.55
).

Appendix H
Finger tensor
F.F^{T}
(left Cauchy–Green tensor)

Finger’s approach is consistent with the foundations of relativity (Galileo classical relativity or Einstein general relativity):
We can only do measurements at the current time
t
, and we can refer to the past.

There is a lot of misunderstandings, as was the case for the Cauchy–Green deformation tensor
C
, due to the lack of precise definitions:
Definition domain? Value domain? Points at stake (
p
or
P
)? Euclidean dot product (English? French?)?
Covariance? Contravariance?…

H.1
Definition

Let
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
be motion,
{t_{0}}\in\mathbb{R}
,
{\Phi^{t_{0}}}
the associated motion,
P\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
,

t\in\mathbb{R}
, and
{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P):=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t_{0}}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t}}})
. And let
(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
be Euclidean dot products in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t_{0}}}}
and
{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t}}}
.

Definition H.1

The Finger tensor
{\underline{\underline{b}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t}})
, or left Cauchy–Green deformation tensor,
at
t
at
{p_{t}}
relative to
{t_{0}}
is the endomorphism
\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t}}})
defined by, with
P={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}^{-1}({p_{t}})
,

{\underline{\underline{b}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t}}):={F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P).({F^{t_{0}}_{t}})_{Gg}^{T}({p_{t}})\quad\hbox{written in short}\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle b=F.F^{T}$},

(H.1)

i.e. is defined by

({\underline{\underline{b}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t}}).{{\vec{w}}_{1}},{{\vec{w}}_{2}})_{g}=({F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)^{T}.{{\vec{w}}_{1}},{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)^{T}.{{\vec{w}}_{2}})_{G}=(({F^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{T}({p_{t}}).{{\vec{w}}_{1}},({F^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{T}({p_{t}}).{{\vec{w}}_{2}})_{G},

for all
{{\vec{w}}_{1}},{{\vec{w}}_{2}}
vectors at
{p_{t}}\in{\Omega_{t}}
, written in short

({\underline{\underline{b}}}.{\vec{w}}_{1},{\vec{w}}_{2})_{g}=(F^{T}.{\vec{w}}_{1},F^{T}.{\vec{w}}_{2})_{G}.

(H.2)

(To compare with
C=F^{T}.F
and
(C.{\vec{W}}_{1},{\vec{W}}_{2})_{G}=(F.{\vec{W}}_{1},F.{\vec{W}}_{2})_{g}
.)

And the Finger tensor relative to
{t_{0}}
is

{\underline{\underline{b}}^{t_{0}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\hbox{\large$\cal C$}=\bigcup_{t}(\{t\}\times{\Omega_{t}})&\rightarrow{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t}}})\cr(t,{p_{t}})&\rightarrow{\underline{\underline{b}}^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t}}):={\underline{\underline{b}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t}}).\crcr}}\,\right.

(H.3)

NB:
{\underline{\underline{b}}^{t_{0}}}
looks like a Eulerian function, but isn’t, since it depends on a
{t_{0}}
.

Other definition found:

{B^{t_{0}}_{t}}:={\underline{\underline{b}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}\circ({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{-1},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad{B^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P):={\underline{\underline{b}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t}})={F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P).{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)^{T},\quad\hbox{written}\quad B=F.F^{T}.

(H.4)

Pay attention:
{B^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t}}})
is an endomorphism at
t
at
{p_{t}}
, not at
{t_{0}}
at
P
:
E.g.,
{B^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P).{\vec{w}}_{t}({p_{t}})={\underline{\underline{b}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t}}).{\vec{w}}_{t}({p_{t}})
is meaningful, while
{B^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P).{\vec{W}}_{t_{0}}(P)
is absurd.

Remark H.2

The push-forward by
\Phi:={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
of the Cauchy–Green deformation tensor
C=F^{T}.F
is

\Phi_{*}(C)=F.C.F^{-1}=F.F^{T}={\underline{\underline{b}}}
, cf. (
8.15
): It is the Finger tensor.
So the endomorphism
C
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
is the pull-back of the endomorphism
{\underline{\underline{b}}}
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
.
(However a push-forward and a pull-back don’t depend on any inner dot product while the transposed
F^{T}
does…).

H.2
{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}

With pull-backs (towards the virtual power principle at
t
).
With
{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
and
{\vec{W}}_{i}(P)=({F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P))^{-1}.{\vec{w}}_{i}({p_{t}})
:

({\vec{W}}_{1},{\vec{W}}_{2})_{G}=(F^{-1}.{\vec{w}}_{1},F^{-1}.{\vec{w}}_{2})_{G}=(F^{-T}.F^{-1}.{\vec{w}}_{1},{\vec{w}}_{2})_{g}=({\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}.{\vec{w}}_{1},{\vec{w}}_{2})_{g}.

(H.5)

So
{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}:=({\underline{\underline{b}}^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{-1}
is useful:

({\underline{\underline{b}}^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{-1}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\Omega_{t}}&\rightarrow{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})\cr{p_{t}}&\rightarrow({\underline{\underline{b}}^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{-1}({p_{t}})={F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)^{-T}.{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)^{-1}={H^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t}})^{T}.{H^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t}})\crcr}}\,\right.

(H.6)

with
{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
and
{H^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t}})=({F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P))^{-1}
cf. (
4.41
). Thus we can define

({\underline{\underline{b}}^{t_{0}}})^{-1}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\bigcup_{t}(\{t\}\times{\Omega_{t}})&\rightarrow{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})\cr(t,{p_{t}})&\rightarrow({\underline{\underline{b}}^{t_{0}}})^{-1}(t,{p_{t}}):=({\underline{\underline{b}}^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{-1}({p_{t}}).\crcr}}\,\right.

(H.7)

Remark:
({\underline{\underline{b}}^{t_{0}}})^{-1}
looks like a Eulerian function, but isn’t, since it depends on
{t_{0}}
.

In short:

{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}=H^{T}.H,\quad\hbox{to compare with}\quad C=F^{T}.F,

(H.8)

and with
{\vec{w}}=F.{\vec{W}}
,

{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}.{\vec{w}}=H^{T}.{\vec{W}},\quad\hbox{to compare with}\quad C.{\vec{W}}=F^{T}.{\vec{w}},

(H.9)

and with
{\vec{W}}_{i}=F^{-1}.{\vec{w}}_{i}
, i.e.
{\vec{w}}_{i}=F.{\vec{W}}_{i}
,

({\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}.{\vec{w}}_{1},{\vec{w}}_{2})_{g}=({\vec{W}}_{1},{\vec{W}}_{2})_{G},\quad\hbox{to compare with}\quad(C.{\vec{W}}_{1},{\vec{W}}_{2})_{G}=({\vec{w}}_{1},{\vec{w}}_{2})_{g}.

(H.10)

Remark H.3

{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
and
b({p_{t}})=F(P).F(P)^{T}
and
C(P)=F(P)^{T}.F(P)
give

{\underline{\underline{b}}}({p_{t}}).F(P)=F(P).C(P),

(H.11)

written
{\underline{\underline{b}}}=F.C.F^{-1}
. Thus
{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}=F.C^{-1}.F^{-1}
, so

{\Phi^{{t_{0}}*}_{t}}{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}=F^{-1}.{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}.F=F^{-1}.F^{-T}=(F^{T}.F)^{-1}=C^{-1},

(H.12)

i.e. the pull-back of
{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}
is
C^{-1}
, i.e.
{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}
is the push-forward of
C^{-1}
.

H.3
Time derivatives of
{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}

With (
H.7
) let
({\underline{\underline{b}}^{t_{0}}})^{-1}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}=H^{T}.H
.
Thus, along a trajectory, and with (
4.45
), we get

\displaystyle{{D{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}\over Dt}=}

\displaystyle{{}{DH^{T}\over Dt}.H+H^{T}.{DH\over Dt}=-d{\vec{v}}^{T}.H^{T}.H-H^{T}.H.d{\vec{v}}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}-{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}.d{\vec{v}}-d{\vec{v}}^{T}.{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}.}

(H.13)

Exercice H.4

Prove (
H.13
) with (
H.10
).

Answer
. (
H.10
) gives

{D\over Dt}({\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}.{\vec{w}}_{1},{\vec{w}}_{2})_{g}=0=({D{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}\over Dt}.{\vec{w}}_{1},{\vec{w}}_{2})_{g}+({\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}.{D{\vec{w}}_{1}\over Dt},{\vec{w}}_{2})_{g}+({\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}.{\vec{w}}_{1},{D{\vec{w}}_{2}\over Dt})_{g}
,
and
{\vec{w}}_{i}(t,p(t))={F^{t_{0}}}(t,P).{\vec{W}}{t_{0}}(P)
gives
{D{\vec{w}}_{i}\over Dt}=d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}_{i}
,
thus

({D{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}\over Dt}.{\vec{w}}_{1},{\vec{w}}_{2})_{g}+({\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}.d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}_{1},{\vec{w}}_{2})_{g}+({\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}.{\vec{w}}_{1},d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}_{2})_{g}=0
,
thus (
H.13
).

Exercice H.5

Prove (
H.13
) with
F^{T}.{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}.F=I_{t_{0}}
.

Answer
.
{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}=(F.F^{T})^{-1}=F^{-T}.F^{-1}
gives
F^{T}.{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}.F=I_{t_{0}}
,
thus
(F^{T})^{\prime}.{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}.F+F^{T}.{D{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}\over Dt}.F+F^{T}.{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}.F^{\prime}=0
,
thus

F^{T}.d{\vec{v}}^{T}.{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}.F+F^{T}.{D{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}\over Dt}.F+F^{T}.{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}.d{\vec{v}}.F=0
,
thus (
H.13
).

H.4
Euler–Almansi tensor
{\underline{\underline{a}}}

Euler–Almansi approach is consistent with the foundations of relativity (Galileo relativity or Einstein general relativity): We can only do measurements at the current time
t
, and we can refer to the past.

At
t
in
{\Omega_{t}}
, consider the Finger tensor
{\underline{\underline{b}}}=F.F^{T}

and its inverse
{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}=F^{-T}.F^{T}=H^{T}.H
cf. (
H.8
).

Definition H.6

Euler–Almansi tenor at
{p_{t}}\in{\Omega_{t}}
is the endomorphism
{\underline{\underline{a}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t}})\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t}}})
defined by

{\underline{\underline{a}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t}})={1\over 2}(I_{t}-{\underline{\underline{b}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t}})^{-1})={1\over 2}(I_{t}-H({p_{t}})^{T}.H({p_{t}})),

(H.14)

written

{\underline{\underline{a}}}={1\over 2}(I-{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1})={1\over 2}(I-H^{T}.H),

(H.15)

to compare with the Green–Lagrange tensor
E={1\over 2}(C-I)={1\over 2}(F^{T}.F-I)\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t_{0}}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t_{0}}}})
.

Remark:
{\underline{\underline{a}}^{t_{0}}}
looks like a Eulerian function, but isn’t, since it depends on
{t_{0}}
.

(
H.10
) gives (
{\vec{w}}_{i}=F.{\vec{W}}_{i}
)

({\vec{w}}_{1},{\vec{w}}_{2})_{g}-({\vec{W}}_{1},{\vec{W}}_{2})_{G}=2({\underline{\underline{a}}}.{\vec{w}}_{1},{\vec{w}}_{2})_{g},

(H.16)

to compare with
({\vec{w}}_{1},{\vec{w}}_{2})_{g}-({\vec{W}}_{1},{\vec{W}}_{2})_{G}=2(E.{\vec{W}}_{1},{\vec{W}}_{2})_{G}
.
(This also gives
({\underline{\underline{a}}}.{\vec{w}}_{1},{\vec{w}}_{2})_{g}=(E.{\vec{W}}_{1},{\vec{W}}_{2})_{G}
.)
And (
H.15
) gives

F^{T}.{\underline{\underline{a}}}.F=E,\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad{\underline{\underline{a}}}=F^{-T}.E.F^{-1},

(H.17)

standing for
{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)^{T}.{\underline{\underline{a}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}(p).{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)={E^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
when
p={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
.

Remark H.7

{\underline{\underline{a}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}
is not the push-forward of
{E^{t_{0}}_{t}}
by
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
(the push-forward is
F.E.F^{-1}
).

H.5
Time Taylor expansion for
{\underline{\underline{a}}}

(
H.13
) gives

{D{\underline{\underline{a}}}\over Dt}={{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}.d{\vec{v}}+d{\vec{v}}^{T}.{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}\over 2}.

(H.18)

H.6
Almansi modified Infinitesimal strain tensor
{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}

We are at
t
(present time) and remember the past: We prefer a definition of a infinitesimal strain tensor
{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}
from the Euler–Almansi tensor
{\underline{\underline{a}}}
,
instead of
{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}
from Euler–Lagrange tensor
E
, cf. §
G.8.2
.

Same Euclidean framework as in §
G.8.2
, and matrix meaning again.

We have
I-{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}=I-H^{T}.H=-(I-H^{T}).(I-H)+2I-H^{T}-H
where
H
stands for
{H^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t}})
.
Thus, for small displacement we get

I-{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}=2I-H^{T}-H+O(h)
,
so

{\underline{\underline{a}}}(t,p(t))=\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}(t,p(t))+O(h)\quad\hbox{where}\quad\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}:=I-{H+H^{T}\over 2}.

(H.19)

And, with
t={t_{0}}+h
we have
{F^{t_{0}}}(t,P)=I+(t{-}{t_{0}})\,d{\vec{v}}(t,P)+o(t{-}{t_{0}})
, cf. (
4.35
),
thus we have
{H^{t_{0}}}(t,p(t))={F^{t_{0}}}(t,P)^{-1}=I-(t{-}{t_{0}})\,d{\vec{v}}(t,P)+o(t{-}{t_{0}})
when
p(t)={\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,P)
.
Thus

{F^{t_{0}}}(t,P)-I=I-{H^{t_{0}}}(t,p(t))+O(t{-}{t_{0}}).

(H.20)

Therefore, for small displacements (
|t-{t_{0}}|<<1
):

{\underline{\underline{a}}}(t,p(t))\simeq\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}(t,p(t))\simeq{{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}^{t_{0}}}}(t,P)\quad\hbox{(matrix meaning)}.

(H.21)

Appendix I
Polar decomposition, elasticity and objectivity

I.1
Polar decompositions of
F
(“isometric objectivity”)

The motion is supposed regular,
{t_{0}},t\in\mathbb{R}
,
{p_{t_{0}}}\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
,
F:={F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})
(
=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})
),
(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
are Euclidean dot products in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
and
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
, and
C=F^{T}.F\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}})
.
Here the covariant objectivity is abandoned due to the need for inner dot products.

I.1.1
F=R.U
(right polar decomposition)

The endomorphism
C
being
(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}
-symmetric definite positive (the motion is supposed to be regular),

\exists\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
(the eigenvalues),
\exists{\vec{c}}_{1},...,{\vec{c}}_{n}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
(associated eigenvectors), such that, for all
i=1,...,n
,

C.{\vec{c}}_{i}=\alpha_{i}{\vec{c}}_{i}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\hbox{$({\vec{c}}_{i})$ is a $(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}$-orthonormal basis in\penalty\ ${\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}$}.

(I.1)

So, if
({\vec{a}}_{i})
is a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}
-Euclidean basis then
D=P^{-1}.[C]_{{\vec{a}}}.P
,
where
D={\rm diag}(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n})=[C]_{{\vec{c}}}
and
P^{-1}=P^{T}
,
P=[P_{ij}]
being the transition matrix from
({\vec{a}}_{i})
to
({\vec{c}}_{i})
, i.e. defined by
{\vec{c}}_{j}=\sum_{i}P_{ij}{\vec{a}}_{i}
for all
j
.

Then, define the endomorphism
U\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}})
, called the right stretch tensor, by, for all
i=1,...,n
,

U.{\vec{c}}_{i}=\sqrt{\alpha_{i}}\,{\vec{c}}_{i},\quad\hbox{and}\quad U{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\sqrt{C},

(I.2)

the
\sqrt{\alpha_{i}}
being called the principal stretches.
Then, define the linear map
R\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})
, called the rotation map, by

R:=F\circ U^{-1}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}F.U^{-1},

(I.3)

so that

\framebox{$\displaystyle F=R\circ U$}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}R.U,\quad\hbox{called the right polar decomposition of\penalty\ $F$}.

(I.4)

Proposition I.1

We have:

C=U\circ U{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}U^{2},\quad\hbox{$U$ is symmetric definite positive},\quad R^{-1}=R^{T}.

(I.5)

And

\hbox{the right polar decomposition}\quad F=R\circ U\quad\hbox{is unique}:

(I.6)

If
F=\widehat{R}\circ\widehat{U}
where
\widehat{U}\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}})
is symmetric definite positive and
\widehat{R}\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})
satisfies
\widehat{R}^{-1}=\widehat{R}^{T}
,
then
\widehat{U}=U
and
\widehat{R}=R
.

Proof.

(
I.2
) yields
(U\circ U).{\vec{c}}_{j}=\lambda{\vec{c}}_{j}=C.{\vec{c}}_{j}
for all
j
, cf. (
I.1
), thus
U\circ U=C
;
Then
(U^{T}.{\vec{c}}_{i},{\vec{c}}_{j})_{G}=({\vec{c}}_{i},U.{\vec{c}}_{j})_{G}=({\vec{c}}_{i},\sqrt{\alpha_{j}}{\vec{c}}_{j})_{G}=\sqrt{\alpha_{j}}({\vec{c}}_{i},{\vec{c}}_{j})_{G}=\sqrt{\alpha_{j}}\delta_{ij}=\sqrt{\alpha_{i}}\delta_{ij}=\sqrt{\alpha_{i}}({\vec{c}}_{i},{\vec{c}}_{j})_{G}=(\sqrt{\alpha_{i}}{\vec{c}}_{i},{\vec{c}}_{j})_{G}=(U.{\vec{c}}_{i},{\vec{c}}_{j})_{G}
for all
i,j
, thus
U^{T}=U
(symmetry).

Then

R^{T}\circ R=U^{-T}\circ F^{T}\circ F\circ U^{-1}=U^{-T}\circ C\circ U^{-1}=U^{-1}\circ(U\circ U)\circ U^{-1}=I_{t_{0}}
identity in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
.
(Details:

(R^{T}.R.{\vec{W}},{\vec{Z}})_{G}=(R.{\vec{W}},R.{\vec{Z}})_{g}=(F.U^{-1}.{\vec{W}},F.U^{-1}.{\vec{Z}})_{g}=(F^{T}.F.U^{-1}.{\vec{W}},U^{-1}.{\vec{Z}})_{G}=(U^{2}.U^{-1}.{\vec{W}},U^{-1}.{\vec{Z}})_{G}=(U^{-1}.U.{\vec{W}},{\vec{Z}})_{G}=({\vec{W}},{\vec{Z}})_{G}
.)
Thus
R^{-1}=R^{T}\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}})
, thus
R\circ R^{T}=R\circ R^{-1}=I_{t}
identity in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
.

And
F=\widehat{R}\circ\widehat{U}=R\circ U
gives

F^{T}\circ F=\widehat{U}^{T}\circ\widehat{R}^{T}\circ\widehat{R}\circ\widehat{U}=U^{T}\circ\widehat{R}^{T}\circ\widehat{R}\circ U
, thus

F^{T}\circ F=\widehat{U}^{T}\circ\widehat{U}
, with
F^{T}\circ F=U^{T}\circ U
, thus
\widehat{U}\circ\widehat{U}=U\circ U=\sqrt{C}
, thus

\widehat{U}=U
(uniqueness of the positive square root eigenvalues). Hence
\widehat{R}=R
.

I.1.2
F=S.R_{0}.U
(shifted right polar decomposition for covariant objectivity)

In fact we need to be more specific if the gift of ubiquity is prohibited:
Since we work with the affine space
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
, consider the Marsden’s shifter

S:={S^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}):\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr T_{\!{p_{t_{0}}}}\!({\Omega_{t_{0}}}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow T_{\!{p_{t}}}\!({\Omega_{t}}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}\cr{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}}}&\rightarrow(S.{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}}})(t,{p_{t}})={\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}}}\quad\hbox{where}\quad{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).\cr}}\,\right.

(I.7)

NB: 1-
S
looks like the algebraic identity if you have time and space ubiquity gift (otherwise it is not),

2-
S
is not a topological identity since it changes the norms in the general case: You consider
||{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}}}||_{G}
at
{t_{0}}
and
||S.{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}}}||_{g}=||{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}}}||_{g}
at
t
.

Then, let
R_{0}\in{\cal L}(T_{\!{p_{t_{0}}}}\!({\Omega_{t_{0}}});T_{\!{p_{t_{0}}}}\!({\Omega_{t_{0}}})){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}})
be the endomorphism defined by, in short,

R_{0}:=S^{-1}\circ R{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}S^{-1}.R,\quad\hbox{so}\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle R=S.R_{0}$}\quad(=S\circ R_{0}).

(I.8)

Full notations:
(R_{0})^{t_{0}}_{t,Gg}({p_{t_{0}}}):=({S^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{-1}(R^{t_{0}}_{t,Gg}({p_{t_{0}}}))
.

Proposition I.2

The endomorphism
R_{0}=S^{-1}\circ R
is a rotation operator in
({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}},(\cdot,\cdot)_{G})
:

R_{0}^{-1}=R_{0}^{T}\quad\hbox{in }({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}},(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}).

(I.9)

And

F=S\circ R_{0}\circ U.

(I.10)

Interpretation:
F
is composed of: The pure deformation
U
(endomorphism in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
), the rotation
R_{0}
(endomorphism in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
), and the shift operator
S:{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
(from past to present time and position).

Proof.

\displaystyle{(R_{0}^{T}.{\vec{W}}_{2},{\vec{W}}_{1})_{G}=}

\displaystyle{{}(R_{0}.{\vec{W}}_{1},{\vec{W}}_{2})_{G}\quad\hbox{(definition of the transposed)}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}(S^{-1}.R.{\vec{W}}_{1},{\vec{W}}_{2})_{G}\quad\hbox{(definition of $R_{0}$)}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}(R.{\vec{W}}_{1},{\vec{W}}_{2})_{G}\quad\hbox{($S$ is the algebraic identity)}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}(R^{T}.{\vec{W}}_{2},{\vec{W}}_{1})_{g}\quad\hbox{(definition of $R^{T}$)}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}(R^{-1}.{\vec{W}}_{2},{\vec{W}}_{1})_{g}\quad\hbox{(cf{.}\penalty\ (\ref{eqFeRUu0}) )}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}(R_{0}^{-1}.{\vec{W}}_{2},{\vec{W}}_{1})_{G}\quad\hbox{($S$ is the algebraic identity)},}

(I.11)

true for all
{\vec{W}}_{1},{\vec{W}}_{2}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
, thus
R_{0}^{T}=R_{0}^{-1}
in
({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}},(\cdot,\cdot)_{G})
.
And (
I.8
) and (
I.4
) give (
I.10
).

Exercice I.3

Let
D={\rm diag}(\alpha_{i})
, let
({\vec{a}}_{i})
be a Euclidean basis in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
,
let
P
be the transition matrix from
({\vec{a}}_{i})
to
({\vec{c}}_{i})
, so
[C]_{|{\vec{a}}}=P.D.P^{-1}
;
Prove
[U]_{|{\vec{a}}}=P.\sqrt{D}.P^{-1}
. Case
({\vec{a}}_{i})=({\vec{E}}_{i})
is a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-orthonormal basis?

Answer
. The
n
equations (
I.1
) (for
j=1,...,n
), read as the matrix equation
[C]_{|{\vec{a}}}.P=P.D
since
[{\vec{c}}_{j}]_{{\vec{a}}}
is the
j
-th column of
P
.
And he
n
equations (
I.2
) (for
j=1,...,n
), read as the matrix equation
[U]_{|{\vec{a}}}.P=P.\sqrt{D}
since
[{\vec{c}}_{j}]_{{\vec{a}}}
is the
j
-th column of
P
.

Remark I.4

Instead of
R_{0}\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}})
, cf. (
I.8
), you may prefer to consider
\widetilde{R}_{0}\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})
defined by

R=\widetilde{R}_{0}\circ S
, i.e.,
\widetilde{R}_{0}=R\circ S^{-1}
.

I.1.3
F=V.R
(left polar decomposition)

Same steps than for the right polar decomposition, but with pull-backs (with
F^{-1}
instead of
F
).

Let
{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})\in{\Omega_{t}}
,
let
{\underline{\underline{b}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t}}):={F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})\circ({F^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{T}({p_{t}})\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})
, written

{\underline{\underline{b}}}=F\circ F^{T}
(the left Cauchy–Green deformation tensor also called the Finger tensor). The endomorphism
b
being symmetric definite positive:

\exists\beta_{1},...,\beta_{n}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
(the eigenvalues),
\exists{\vec{d}}_{1},...,{\vec{d}}_{n}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
(associated eigenvectors), such that, for all
i=1,...,n
,

{\underline{\underline{b}}}.{\vec{d}}_{i}=\beta_{i}{\vec{d}}_{i},\quad\hbox{and}\quad\hbox{$({\vec{d}}_{i})$ is a $(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}$-orthonormal basis in\penalty\ ${\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}$}.

(I.12)

Then, define the unique endomorphism
V\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})
, called the left stretch tensor, by, for all
i=1,...,n
,

V.{\vec{d}}_{i}=\sqrt{\beta_{i}}{\vec{d}}_{i},\quad\hbox{and}\quad V{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\sqrt{\underline{\underline{b}}}.

(I.13)

(Full notation:
V^{t_{0}}_{t,Gg}({p_{t}})=\sqrt{{\underline{\underline{b}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t}})_{Gg}}
.)
Then define the linear map
R_{\ell}\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})
by

R_{\ell}:=V^{-1}\circ F{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}V^{-1}.F,

(I.14)

so that

\framebox{$\displaystyle F=V\circ R_{\ell}$}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}V.R_{\ell},\quad\hbox{called the left polar decomposition of\penalty\ $F$}.

(I.15)

Proposition I.5

We have: 1-

{\underline{\underline{b}}}=V\circ V{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}V^{2},\quad\hbox{$V$ is symmetric definite positive},\quad R_{\ell}^{-1}=R_{\ell}^{T}.

(I.16)

And the left polar decomposition
F=V\circ R
is unique.

2-
R_{\ell}=R
and
V=R.U.R^{-1}
(so
U
and
V
are similar), thus
U
and
V
have the same eigenvalues, i.e.,
\alpha_{i}=\beta_{i}
for all
i
, and
{\vec{d}}_{i}=R.{\vec{c}}_{i}
for all
i
gives a relation between eigenvectors.

Proof.
1- Use
F^{-1}
and
{\underline{\underline{b}}}^{-1}=(F^{-1})^{T}.(F^{-1})
, instead of
F
and
C=F^{T}.F
, to get
F^{-1}=R_{\ell}^{-1}.U_{\ell}^{-1}
, cf. (
I.3
); Thus
F=U_{\ell}.R_{\ell}
; Then name
U_{\ell}=V
to get (
I.15
) and (
I.16
).

2-
V.R_{\ell}=F=R.U=(R.U.R^{-1}).R
,
thus, by uniqueness of the right polar decomposition,
V=R.U.R^{-1}
(so
U
and
V
are similar) and
R_{\ell}=R
. Thus,
with (
I.12
),

\beta_{i}{\vec{d}}_{i}=V.{\vec{d}}_{i}=R.U.(R^{-1}.{\vec{d}}_{i})
, thus with
{\vec{c}}_{i}=R^{-1}.{\vec{d}}_{i}
, then
({\vec{c}}_{i})
is an orthonormal basis in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}

and

\beta_{i}R.{\vec{c}}_{i}=R.U.{\vec{c}}_{i}=\alpha_{i}R.{\vec{c}}_{i}
gives
\beta_{i}=\alpha_{i}
and the
{\vec{c}}_{i}
are eigenvectors of
U
, for all
i
.

I.2
Linear elasticity: A Classical “tensorial” approach

I.2.1
Classical approach (“isometric objectivity”), and an issue

With the infinitesimal strain “tensor” (which is not a tensor but a matrix)

{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}={F+F^{T}\over 2}-I,

(I.17)

the homogeneous isotropic elasticity constitutive law reads (matrix equation for the stress)

({\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}(\Phi)=)\quad{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}=\lambda{\rm Tr}({\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}})I+2\mu{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}},

(I.18)

where
\lambda,\mu
are the Lamé coefficients and
{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}
is the Cauchy stress “tensor”.

Issue:
Recall: Adding
F
and
F^{T}
to make
{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}
is functionally a mathematical nonsense since

F:{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
and
F^{T}:{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}

and
I
is some identity operator:
{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}
is not a tensor.
In particular the meaning of
{\rm Tr}({\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}})
is questionable (since
{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}
is not an endomorphism
and
{\rm Tr}({\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}})
means
{\rm Tr}([{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}])={{\rm Tr}([F])+{\rm Tr}([F^{T}])\over 2}-n
),
as well as the meaning of
{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}.{\vec{n}}={1\over 2}(F.{\vec{n}}+F^{T}.{\vec{n}})
, or the meaning of

{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}.{\vec{n}}=\lambda{\rm Tr}({\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}){\vec{n}}+2\mu{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}.{\vec{n}}

(I.19)

since
{\vec{n}}
has to be defined at
({t_{0}},{p_{t_{0}}})
for
F
and at
(t,{p_{t}})
for
F^{T}
.
(Cauchy’s approach:
{\vec{n}}
is defined at
(t,{p_{t}})
.)

So, despite the eventual claims, neither
{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}
nor
{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}
are tensors (they don’t have a functional meaning): They only have a questionable matrix meaning (observer dependent)
[{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}]:={[F]{+}[F]^{T}\over 2}-[I]
and

[{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]=\lambda{\rm Tr}([{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}])[I]+2\mu[{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}],\quad\hbox{and}\quad[{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}].[{\vec{n}}]=\lambda{\rm Tr}([{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}])[{\vec{n}}]+2\mu[{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}].[{\vec{n}}].

(I.20)

Remark I.6

To justify the name “tensor” applied to
{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}
, you may read: “For small displacements the Eulerian variable
{p_{t}}
and the Lagrangian variable
{p_{t_{0}}}
can be confused”:
{p_{t}}\simeq{p_{t_{0}}}
(so
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
and
{\Omega_{t}}
are “almost equal”,
so
F({p_{t_{0}}})+F^{T}({p_{t}})
can be considered). Which means that you use the zero-th order Taylor expansions
{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t)={p_{t_{0}}}+o(1)
. But then, you can
not
also use the first (or higher) order Taylor expansion in following calculations, e.g. you cannot use velocities…

I.2.2
A functional (tensorial) formulation (“isometric objectivity”)

Question: Can the constitutive law (
I.18
) be modified into a tensorial expression (a functional expression)?
Proposal for a yes:

1.

Consider the “right polar decomposition”
F=R.U
where
U\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}})
, cf. (
I.3
).
The Green Lagrange tensor
E={C{-}I\over 2}
(endomorphism in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
) then reads, with (
I.5
),

E={U^{2}{-}I_{t_{0}}\over 2}={(U{-}I_{t_{0}})^{2}+2(U-I_{t_{0}})\over 2}

(I.21)

(the Green–Lagrange tensor is independent of the rotation
R
),
thus, with
U-I_{t_{0}}=O(h)
(small deformation approximation), we get the modified infinitesimal strain tensor at
{p_{t_{0}}}\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}

\framebox{$\displaystyle{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}=U{-}I_{t_{0}}$}\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}),

(I.22)

endomorphism in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
(to compare with
{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}
which is not a function, cf. the previous §).
(Full notation
{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}^{t_{0}}_{t,Gg}({p_{t_{0}}})=U^{t_{0}}_{t,Gg}({p_{t_{0}}}){-}I_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}})
in
{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}})
.)
And, for all
{\vec{W}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
we get

\framebox{$\displaystyle{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}.{\vec{W}}=U.{\vec{W}}-{\vec{W}}=R^{-1}.{\vec{w}}-{\vec{W}}$}\;\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}},\quad\hbox{when}\quad{\vec{w}}=F.{\vec{W}}\hbox{ (push-forward)}.

(I.23)

Interpretation
: From
{\vec{w}}=F.{\vec{W}}=R.U.{\vec{W}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
(the deformed by the motion), remove the “rigid body rotation” to get
R^{-1}.{\vec{w}}=U.{\vec{W}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
, to which you remove the initial
{\vec{W}}
to obtain
{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}.{\vec{W}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
.
In particular
||{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}.{\vec{W}}||_{G}=||(U{-}I_{t_{0}}).{\vec{W}}||_{G}
measures the relative elongation undergone by
{\vec{W}}
.
And you can then apply
R
to get back into
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
at
{p_{t}}
:

R.({\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}.{\vec{W}})=F.{\vec{W}}-R.{\vec{W}}={\vec{w}}-R.{\vec{W}}\;\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}},\quad\hbox{when}\quad{\vec{w}}=F.{\vec{W}}=\hbox{ (push-forward)}.

(I.24)

2.

Then, at
{p_{t_{0}}}\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
, consider the stress tensor
\widetilde{\Sigma}(\Phi){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\widetilde{\Sigma}\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}})
(functionally well) defined by

\framebox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\Sigma}=\lambda{\rm Tr}({\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}})I_{t_{0}}+2\mu{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}$}=\lambda{\rm Tr}(U{-}I_{t_{0}})I_{t_{0}}+2\mu(U{-}I_{t_{0}}).

(I.25)

(The trace
{\rm Tr}({\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}})
is well defined since
{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}
is an endomorphism.)
Then for any
{\vec{W}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
you get in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
, at
{p_{t_{0}}}\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
,

\widetilde{\Sigma}.{\vec{W}}=\lambda{\rm Tr}({\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}){\vec{W}}+2\mu{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}.{\vec{W}}=\lambda{\rm Tr}(U{-}I_{t_{0}}){\vec{W}}+2\mu(U.{\vec{W}}{-}{\vec{W}})

(I.26)

(functionally well defined in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
). Then rotate and shift with
R
to get into
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
at
{p_{t}}
,

\displaystyle{R.\widetilde{\Sigma}.{\vec{W}}=}

\displaystyle{{}\lambda{\rm Tr}({\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}})R.{\vec{W}}+2\mu R.{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}.{\vec{W}}=\lambda{\rm Tr}(U{-}I_{t_{0}})R.{\vec{W}}+2\mu R.(U{-}I_{t_{0}}).{\vec{W}}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}\lambda{\rm Tr}(U{-}I_{t_{0}})R.{\vec{W}}+2\mu(F-R).{\vec{W}},}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}\lambda{\rm Tr}(U{-}I_{t_{0}})R.{\vec{W}}+2\mu({\vec{w}}-R.{\vec{W}}),\quad\hbox{where}\quad{\vec{w}}=F.{\vec{W}}=R.U.{\vec{W}}.}

(I.27)

You have defined the two point tensor (functionally well defined)

R.\widetilde{\Sigma}=\lambda{\rm Tr}({\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}})R+2\mu R.{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}\;\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}).

(I.28)

3.

Then you can propose the constitutive law with the stress tensor (the symmetric endomorphism) in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
given by

({\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}}(\Phi)=)\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}}=R\circ\widetilde{\Sigma}\circ R^{-1}$}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}R.\widetilde{\Sigma}.R^{-1}\;\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}).

(I.29)

(Functionally well defined.) And, for all vector fields
{\vec{w}}
defined in
{\Omega_{t}}
, you get the (functionally well defined) vector field

{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}}.{\vec{w}}=R.\widetilde{\Sigma}.R^{-1}.{\vec{w}}\;\;\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}.

(I.30)

Interpretation
of (
I.29
)-(
I.30
): Shift and rigid rotate backward by applying
R^{-1}
, apply the elastic stress law with
\Sigma
which corresponds to a rotation free motion (Noll’s frame indifference principle), then shift and rigid rotate forward by applying
R
.

Detailed expression for (
I.29
)-(
I.30
):
With
{\rm Tr}(R.{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}.R^{-1})={\rm Tr}({\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}})
(see exercise
I.8
), we get, at
(t,{p_{t}})
,

\displaystyle{{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}}=\lambda{\rm Tr}({\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}})\,I_{t}+2\mu R.{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}.R^{-1}=}

\displaystyle{{}\lambda{\rm Tr}(U{-}I_{t_{0}})\,I_{t}+2\mu R.(U{-}I_{t_{0}}).R^{-1}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}\lambda{\rm Tr}(U{-}I_{t_{0}})\,I_{t}+2\mu(F.R^{-1}{-}I_{t}).}

(I.31)

And for any
{\vec{w}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
, and with
{\vec{w}}=R.{\vec{W}}
, you get

\displaystyle{{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}}.{\vec{w}}=\lambda{\rm Tr}({\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}})\,{\vec{w}}+2\mu R.{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}.{\vec{W}}=}

\displaystyle{{}\lambda{\rm Tr}(U{-}I_{t_{0}})\,{\vec{w}}+2\mu R.(U{-}I_{t_{0}}).{\vec{W}}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}\lambda{\rm Tr}(U{-}I_{t_{0}})\,{\vec{w}}+2\mu(R.U.R^{-1}.{\vec{w}}{-}{\vec{w}}).}

(I.32)

To compare with the classical functionally meaningless (
I.19
).

Remark I.7

Doing so, you avoid the use of the Piola–Kirchhoff tensors.

Exercice I.8

Prove:
{\rm Tr}(R.{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}.R^{-1})={\rm Tr}({\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}})=\sum_{i}(\alpha_{i}{-}1)
.
(NB:
{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}
is an endomorphism in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
while
R.{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}.R^{-1}
is an endomorphism in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
.)

Answer
.
\det_{|{\vec{e}}}(R.{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}.R^{-1}-\lambda I_{t})=\det_{|{\vec{e}}}(R.({\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}{-}\lambda I_{t_{0}}).R^{-1})=\det_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{e}}}(R).\det_{|{\vec{E}}}({\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}{-}\lambda I).\det_{|{\vec{e}},{\vec{E}}}(R^{-1})=\det_{|{\vec{E}}}({\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}{-}\lambda I)
for all Euclidean bases
({\vec{E}}_{i})
and
({\vec{e}}_{i})
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
and
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
.
(With
L={\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}
and components,
{\rm Tr}(R.L.R^{-1})=\sum_{i}(R.L.R^{-1})^{i}_{i}=\sum_{ijk}R^{i}_{j}L^{j}_{k}(R^{-1})^{k}_{i}=\sum_{jk}(R^{-1}.R)^{k}_{j}L^{j}_{k}=\sum_{jk}\delta^{k}_{j}L^{j}_{k}=\sum_{j}L^{j}_{j}={\rm Tr}(L)
.)

Exercice I.9

Elongation in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
along the first axis : origin
O
, same Euclidean basis
({\vec{E}}_{1},{\vec{E}}_{2})
and Euclidean dot product at all time,

\xi>0
,
t\geq{t_{0}}
,
L,H>0
,
P\in[0,L]\times[0,H]
,
[\overrightarrow{OP}]_{|{\vec{E}}}=\pmatrix{X_{0}\cr Y_{0}}
, and

[\overrightarrow{O{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)}]_{|{\vec{E}}}=\pmatrix{X_{0}+\xi(t{-}{t_{0}})X_{0}\cr Y_{0}}=\pmatrix{X_{0}(\kappa{+}1)\cr Y_{0}}=\pmatrix{x\cr y}=[\overrightarrow{Op}]_{|{\vec{E}}}
,
where
\kappa=\xi(t{-}{t_{0}})>0
for
t>{t_{0}}
.

1- Give
F
,
C
,
U=\sqrt{C}
and
R=F.U^{-1}
. Relation with the classical expression ?

2- Spring
\overrightarrow{OP}=\overrightarrow{Oc_{t_{0}}}(s)=X_{0}{\vec{E}}_{1}+Y_{0}{\vec{E}}_{2}+s{\vec{W}}
,
i.e.
[\overrightarrow{OP}]_{|{\vec{E}}}=[\overrightarrow{Oc_{t_{0}}}]_{|{\vec{E}}}=\pmatrix{X_{0}{+}sW_{1}\cr Y_{0}{+}sW_{2}}_{|{\vec{E}}}
with
s\in[0,L]
and
{\vec{W}}=W_{1}{\vec{E}}_{1}+W_{2}{\vec{E}}_{2}
.
Give the deformed spring, i.e. give
p=c_{t}(s)={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(c_{t_{0}}(s))
, and
{\vec{c}}_{t}{}^{\prime}
, and the stretch ratio.

Answer
. 1-
[F]=[d\Phi]=\pmatrix{\kappa{+}1&0\cr 0&1}
, same Euclidean dot product and basis at all time,
thus
[F^{T}]=[F]^{T}=[F]
, then
