[C]=[F^{T}].[F]=[F]^{2}=\pmatrix{(\kappa{+}1)^{2}&0\cr 0&1}
,
thus
[U]=[F]=\pmatrix{\kappa{+}1&0\cr 0&1}
, thus
[R]=[I]
.
All the matrices are given relative to the basis
({\vec{E}}_{i})
, thus
F,C,U,R
(e.g.,
C.{\vec{E}}_{1}=(\kappa{+}1)^{2}{\vec{E}}_{1}
and
C.{\vec{E}}_{2}={\vec{E}}_{2}
).

Since
R=I
and
[{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}]=[{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}]
, (
I.31
) gives the usual result

[{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]=\lambda{\rm Tr}([{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}])I+2\mu[{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}]
, cf (
I.18
) (matrix meaning).

2-
\overrightarrow{Oc_{t}(s)}=\overrightarrow{O{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(c_{t_{0}}(s))}=\pmatrix{(X_{0}{+}sW_{1})(\kappa{+}1)\cr Y_{0}{+}sW_{2}}_{|{\vec{E}}}
, thus
{\vec{c}}_{t}{}^{\prime}(s)=\pmatrix{W_{1}(\kappa{+}1)\cr W_{2}}_{|{\vec{E}}}
,
stretch ration
{W_{1}^{2}(\kappa{+}1)^{2}+W_{2}^{2}\over W_{1}^{2}+W_{2}^{2}}
at
(t,{p_{t}})
.

Exercice I.10

Simple shear in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
:

[\overrightarrow{O{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)}]_{|{\vec{E}}}=\pmatrix{X+\xi(t{-}{t_{0}})Y\cr Y}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\pmatrix{X+\kappa Y\cr Y}=\pmatrix{x\cr y}=[\overrightarrow{Op}]_{|{\vec{E}}}
. Same questions, and moreover give the eigenvalues of
C
.

Answer
. 1-
[F]=\pmatrix{1&\kappa\cr 0&1}
,

[C]=\pmatrix{1&0\cr\kappa&1}.\pmatrix{1&\kappa\cr 0&1}=\pmatrix{1&\kappa\cr\kappa&\kappa^{2}{+}1}
. Eigenvalues:

\det(C-\lambda I)=\lambda^{2}-(2{+}\kappa^{2})\lambda+1
.
Discriminant
\Delta=(2{+}\kappa^{2})^{2}-4=\kappa^{2}(\kappa^{2}{+}4)
.
Eigenvalues
\alpha_{\pm}={1\over 2}(2{+}\kappa^{2}\pm\kappa\sqrt{\kappa^{2}{+}4})
.
(We check that
\alpha_{\pm}>0
.)
Eigenvectors
{\vec{v}}_{\pm}
(main directions of deformations) given by
(1{-}\alpha_{\pm})x+\kappa y=0
,
i.e.,
y={1\over 2}(\kappa\pm\sqrt{\kappa^{2}{+}4})x
,
thus, e.g.,

{\vec{v}}_{\pm}=\pmatrix{2\cr\kappa\pm\sqrt{\kappa^{2}{+}4}\cr}
.
(We check that
{\vec{v}}_{+}\perp{\vec{v}}_{-}
.)
With
P
the transition matrix from
({\vec{E}}_{1},{\vec{E}}_{2})
to
({{\vec{v}}_{+}\over||{\vec{v}}_{+}||},{{\vec{v}}_{-}\over||{\vec{v}}_{-}||})
and
D={\rm diag}(\alpha_{+},\alpha_{-})

we get
C=P.D.P^{-1}
(with
P^{-1}=P^{T}
since here
({{\vec{v}}_{+}\over||{\vec{v}}_{+}||},{{\vec{v}}_{-}\over||{\vec{v}}_{-}||})

is an orthonormal basis), thus
U=P.\sqrt{D}.P^{-1}
(we check that
U^{T}=U
and
U^{2}=C
).
And
R=F.U^{-1}
.

2-
\overrightarrow{Oc_{t}(s)}=\overrightarrow{O{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(c_{t_{0}}(s))}=\pmatrix{(X_{0}{+}sW_{1})+\kappa(Y_{0}{+}sW_{2})\cr Y_{0}{+}sW_{2}}
, thus
[{\vec{c}}_{t}{}^{\prime}(s)]=\pmatrix{W_{1}+\kappa W_{2}\cr W_{2}}
.
Stretch ratio
{(W_{1}+\kappa W_{2})^{2}+W_{2}^{2}\over W_{1}^{2}+W_{2}^{2}}
at
(t,{p_{t}})
.

I.2.3
Second functional formulation: With the Finger tensor

The above approach uses the push-forward: It uses
F
, i.e. you arrive with your memory. You may prefer to use the pull-back, i.e. use
F^{-1}
(you remember the past which is Cauchy’s point of view): Then you use
F^{-1}=R^{-1}.V^{-1}
the right polar decomposition of
F^{-1}
, and you consider the tensor

{\widetilde{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}}_{t}=V^{-1}{-}I_{t}\;\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}),

(I.33)

and

({\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}_{t}(\Phi)=)\quad{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}_{t}=\lambda{\rm Tr}({\widetilde{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}}_{t})I_{t}+2\mu{\widetilde{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}}_{t},\quad\hbox{and}\quad{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}_{t}.{\vec{n}}_{t}=\lambda{\rm Tr}({\widetilde{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}}_{t})\,{\vec{n}}_{t}+2\mu{\widetilde{\widetilde{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}}_{t}.{\vec{n}}_{t}.

(I.34)

(Quantities functionally well defined: Give a tensorial approach).

I.3
Elasticity with a covariant objective approach?

In §
I.2
you need to start with Euclidean dot products, so from the start the result can’t be covariant objective. Can you start without Euclidean dot products to set up general laws?
Proposal:

Hypothesis: The Cauchy stress
{\vec{w}}
is a Eulerian vector field.

Then we could use the (covariant objective) Lie derivative which characterizes the rate of stress,
cf. §
9.3
and
9.5
: With a particle
{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}\in{\it O\!b\!j}
, with
{\vec{v}}(\tau,{p_{\tau}})={\partial{\widetilde{\Phi}}\over\partial\tau}(\tau,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
its Eulerian velocity at
\tau
at
{p_{\tau}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}(\tau,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
,
the Lie derivative of a Eulerian vector field
{\vec{w}}
along
{\vec{v}}
is, at
(t,{p_{t}})
,

\displaystyle{{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}(t,{p_{t}})=}

\displaystyle{{}\lim_{\tau\rightarrow t}{{\vec{w}}(\tau,{p_{\tau}})-\vec{w}_{t*}(\tau,{p_{\tau}})\over\tau-t}=({\partial{\vec{w}}\over\partial t}+d{\vec{w}}.{\vec{v}}-d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}})(t,{p_{t}}).}

(I.35)

Hence the proposal, with the virtual power principle to measure the rate of stress (see https://arxiv.org/abs/2208.10780v1 for a full description).

1- Hypotheses:
1.1- Suppose that
n
Eulerian vector fields
{\vec{w}}_{j}
(“force fields”),
j=1,...,n
, enable to characterize a material.
(In fact, for elasticity problems it could be better to replace vector fields
{\vec{w}}_{j}
with 1-forms
\alpha_{i}
to characterize the work.)

1.2-
With a basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
chosen in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
, with
(e^{i})
its (covariant) dual basis in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n*}_{t}}
, assume that the internal power density at
(t,{p_{t}})
is given by (at first order):

p_{\rm int}({\vec{v}})={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}e^{j}.{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}_{j}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}e^{j}.({\partial{\vec{w}}_{j}\over\partial t}+d{\vec{w}}_{j}.{\vec{v}}-d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}_{j}).

(I.36)

(At second order you can add second order Lie derivatives as
{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}({\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}_{j})
,
similarly for higher orders.)

2- Then, so that this
p_{\rm int}
satisfies the frame invariance hypothesis, choose a Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
; Then, first, the internal power has to vanish if
d{\vec{v}}=0
, thus we are left with

p_{\rm int}({\vec{v}})=-{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}e^{j}.d{\vec{v}}.{\vec{w}}_{j}=-{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}d{\vec{v}},\quad\hbox{where}\quad{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}{\vec{w}}_{j}\otimes e^{j},

(I.37)

defined at
t
.
(The
j
-th column of
[{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}
is
[{\vec{w}}_{j}]_{|{\vec{e}}}
.)
And, second, the internal power vanishes if
d{\vec{v}}+d{\vec{v}}^{T}=0
(rotation), thus we are left with

p_{\rm int}({\vec{v}})=-{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}{d{\vec{v}}+d{\vec{v}}^{T}\over 2}=-{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}d{\vec{v}}\quad\hbox{where}\quad{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}={{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}+{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}^{T}\over 2},

(I.38)

this
p_{\rm int}({\vec{v}})=-{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}d{\vec{v}}
being the usual expression of the internal power at first order.

Example I.11

(
I.36
) may be applied to orthotropic elasticity, e.g. for a material which fibers at some time
{t_{0}}
are along
{\vec{e}}_{1}
, in a 2-D case for simplicity: 1- With an elongation type motion
(\Phi_{e})
given by

[(F_{e})({p_{t_{0}}})]=[d(\Phi_{e})({p_{t_{0}}})]=\pmatrix{1{+}\alpha_{11}({p_{t_{0}}})&0\cr 0&1{-}\alpha_{22}({p_{t_{0}}})}

you measure the Young moduli in the directions
{\vec{e}}_{1}
and
{\vec{e}}_{2}
;
2- And with a shear type motion given by

[(F_{s})({p_{t_{0}}})]=[d(\Phi_{s})({p_{t_{0}}})]=\pmatrix{1&\gamma_{12}\cr 0&1}

you measure the shear modulus.

For more complex material, you may need more vectors
{\vec{w}}_{j}
to describe the constitutive law, that is, (
I.36
) may be considered with
{\sum_{i=1}^{m}}e^{j}.{\cal L}_{\vec{v}}{\vec{w}}_{j}
with
m>n
.

Remark I.12

The Lie approach is different from the usual classic approach:

1- The classic approach looks for an order two stress tensor
[{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]
as a function of the deformation gradient
[F]
, cf. (
I.18
).

2- The Lie approach begins with the internal power (which measures forces), cf. (
I.36
), which then enable to build
{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}
and the
{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}
(the stress tensor), cf. (
I.37
)-(
I.38
).

E.g., application to visco-elasticity: With the Lie approach, you automatically use Lie derivative of vector fields (and/or of differential forms), instead of Lie derivative of order 2 tensor fields (which does not seem to give good result, see e.g. the Maxwell visco-elastic type laws, as well as footnote
1
page
1
).

Appendix J
Displacement

J.1
The displacement vector
{\vec{\cal U}}

In
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
, let
{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})
. Then the bi-point vector

{{\vec{\cal U}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})-I_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}})={p_{t}}-{p_{t_{0}}}=\overrightarrow{{p_{t_{0}}}{p_{t}}}

(J.1)

is called the displacement vector at
{p_{t_{0}}}
relative to
t_{0}
and
t
.
This defines the map

{{\vec{\cal U}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\Omega_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\cr{p_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow{{\vec{\cal U}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}):={p_{t}}-{p_{t_{0}}}=\overrightarrow{{p_{t_{0}}}{p_{t}}}\quad\hbox{when}\quad{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).\crcr}}\,\right.

(J.2)

Remark J.1

{{\vec{\cal U}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})
doesn’t define a vector field (it is not tensorial),
because
{{\vec{\cal U}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})={p_{t}}-{p_{t_{0}}}=\overrightarrow{{p_{t_{0}}}{p_{t}}}
is a bi-point vector which is neither in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
or in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}

since
{p_{t_{0}}}\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
and
{p_{t}}\in{\Omega_{t}}
(it requires time and space ubiquity gift). In particular, it makes no sense on a non-plane surface (manifold).
More at §
J.5
.

Remark J.2

For elastic solids in
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
,
the function
{\vec{\cal U}}^{t_{0}}
is often considered to be the unknown (to be computed);
But the “real” unknown is the motion
{\Phi^{t_{0}}}
.
And it is sometimes confused with the extension of a spring
1
-D case; But see figure
4.1
where

||{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}})||
represents the initial length and
||{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}(t,{p_{t}})||
represents the current length of the spring, while the length of the displacement vector
{{\vec{\cal U}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}={p_{t}}-{p_{t_{0}}}
can be very long for a very small elongation
||{\vec{w}}_{{t_{0}}*}(t,{p_{t}})||-||{\vec{w}}_{t_{0}}({p_{t_{0}}})||
of the spring.

J.2
The differential of the displacement vector

The differential of
{{\vec{\cal U}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}
at
{p_{t_{0}}}
is

d{{\vec{\cal U}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})-I_{t_{0}}={F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})-I_{t_{0}},\quad\hbox{written}\quad d{\vec{\cal U}}=F-I,

(J.3)

thus isn’t defined as a function,
because
{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}):{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
while
I_{t_{0}}:{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
.

So
d{{\vec{\cal U}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})
as to be understood as a matrix: With

[{{\vec{\cal U}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})]=[\overrightarrow{{p_{t_{0}}}{p_{t}}}]=[\overrightarrow{O{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})}]-[\overrightarrow{O{p_{t_{0}}}}],

(J.4)

relative to an origin
O
and a unique basis at all time,
compute
[d{{\vec{\cal U}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})]=[d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})]-I
, abusively written
d{\vec{\cal U}}=d\Phi-I
.
Then, with
{\vec{W}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
,

d{\vec{\cal U}}.{\vec{W}}=F.{\vec{W}}-{\vec{W}},\quad\hbox{which means}\quad=[{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})].[{\vec{W}}]-[{\vec{W}}].

(J.5)

Thus we have defined (matrix meaning)

{\vec{\cal U}}^{t_{0}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr[{t_{0}},T]\times{\Omega_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\cr(t,{p_{t_{0}}})&\rightarrow{\vec{\cal U}}^{t_{0}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}):={{\vec{\cal U}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}),\crcr}}\,\right.\quad\hbox{and}\quad{{\vec{\cal U}}^{t_{0}}_{{p_{t_{0}}}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr[{t_{0}},T]&\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\cr t&\rightarrow{{\vec{\cal U}}^{t_{0}}_{{p_{t_{0}}}}}(t):={{\vec{\cal U}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).\crcr}}\,\right.

(J.6)

J.3
Deformation “tensor”
{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}
(matrix), bis

(
J.3
) gives (matrix meaning)

{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})=I_{t_{0}}+d{{\vec{\cal U}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}),\quad\hbox{written}\quad F=I+d{\vec{\cal U}}.

(J.7)

Therefore, Cauchy–Green deformation tensor
C=F^{T}.F
reads, in short, (matrix meaning)

C=I+d{\vec{\cal U}}+d{\vec{\cal U}}^{T}+d{\vec{\cal U}}^{T}.d{\vec{\cal U}}\quad\hbox{(matrix meaning)},

(J.8)

i.e.
[{C^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})]=[I_{t_{0}}]+[d{{\vec{\cal U}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})]+[d{{\vec{\cal U}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})]^{T}+[d{{\vec{\cal U}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})]^{T}.[d{{\vec{\cal U}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})]
.

Thus the Green–Lagrange deformation tensor
E={C-I\over 2}
, cf. (
G.47
), reads, in short, (matrix meaning)

E={d{\vec{\cal U}}+d{\vec{\cal U}}^{T}\over 2}+{1\over 2}d{\vec{\cal U}}^{T}.d{\vec{\cal U}}\quad\hbox{(matrix meaning)}.

(J.9)

Thus the deformation tensor
{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}
, cf. (
G.54
), reads (matrix meaning)

{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}=E-{1\over 2}(d{\vec{\cal U}})^{T}.d{\vec{\cal U}},

(J.10)

with
{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}
the “linear part” of
E
(small displacements: we only used the first order derivative
d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
).

J.4
Small displacement hypothesis, bis

(Usual introduction.)
Let
{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})
,
{\vec{W}}_{i}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t_{0}}}}
,
{\vec{w}}_{i}({p_{t}})={F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{W}}_{i}({p_{t_{0}}})\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
(the push-forwards), written
{\vec{w}}_{i}=F.{\vec{W}}_{i}
.
Then define (matrix meaning)

{\vec{\Delta}}_{i}:={\vec{w}}_{i}-{\vec{W}}_{i}=d{\cal U}.{\vec{W}}_{i},\quad\hbox{and}\quad||{\vec{\Delta}}||_{\infty}=\max(||{\vec{\Delta}}_{1}||_{\mathbb{R}^{n}},||{\vec{\Delta}}_{2}||_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}).

(J.11)

Then the small displacement hypothesis reads (matrix meaning):

||{\vec{\Delta}}||_{\infty}=o(||{\vec{W}}||_{\infty}).

(J.12)

Thus

{\vec{w}}_{i}={\vec{W}}_{i}+{\vec{\Delta}}_{i}
(with
{\vec{\Delta}}_{i}
“small”) and the hypothesis
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}=(\cdot,\cdot)_{G}
(same inner dot product at
{t_{0}}
and
t
) give

({\vec{w}}_{1},{\vec{w}}_{2})_{G}-({\vec{W}}_{1},{\vec{W}}_{2})_{G}=({\vec{\Delta}}_{1},{\vec{W}}_{2})_{G}+({\vec{\Delta}}_{2},{\vec{W}}_{1})_{G}+({\vec{\Delta}}_{1},{\vec{\Delta}}_{2})_{G}.

So (
J.10
) gives

2(E.{\vec{W}}_{1},{\vec{W}}_{2})_{G}=2({\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}.{\vec{W}}_{1},{\vec{W}}_{2})_{G}+(d{\vec{\cal U}}^{T}.d{\vec{\cal U}}.{\vec{W}}_{1},{\vec{W}}_{2})_{G},

And (
J.12
) gives

(E.{\vec{W}}_{1},{\vec{W}}_{2})_{G}=({\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}.{\vec{W}}_{1},{\vec{W}}_{2})_{G}+O(||{\vec{\Delta}}||_{\infty}^{2}),

(J.13)

so
{E^{t_{0}}_{t}}
is approximated by
{{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}}
, that is,
{E^{t_{0}}_{t}}\simeq{{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}}
(matrix meaning).

J.5
Displacement vector with differential geometry

J.5.1
The shifter

We give the steps, see Marsden–Hughes
[
14
]
.
The complexity introduced is due to the small displacement hypothesis applied to the Green–Lagrange tensor
E={F^{T}.F-I\over 2}
which linearization gives
{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}={F+F^{T}\over 2}-I

(the classical approach “squares the motion” to get
E
, then “linearizes”
E
… to get back to
F
… with a spurious
F^{T}
).

Let
P\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
,
{\vec{W}}_{P}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
,
{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)\in{\Omega_{t}}
, and
{\vec{w}}_{p_{t}}={F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P).{\vec{W}}_{P}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
(push-forward).

\bullet
Affine case
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
(continuum mechanics):

With
p_{t}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
, the shifter is:

{\widetilde{S^{t_{0}}_{t}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\Omega_{t_{0}}}\times{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow{\Omega_{t}}\times{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}\cr(P,{\vec{Z}}_{P})&\rightarrow{\widetilde{S^{t_{0}}_{t}}}(P,{\vec{Z}}_{P})=({p_{t}},{S^{t_{0}}_{t}}({\vec{Z}}_{P}))\quad\hbox{with}\quad{S^{t_{0}}_{t}}({\vec{Z}}_{P})={\vec{Z}}_{P}.\crcr}}\,\right.

(J.14)

(The vector is unchanged but the time and the application point have changed: A real observer has no ubiquity gift).
So:

{S^{t_{0}}_{t}}\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})\quad\hbox{and}\quad[{S^{t_{0}}_{t}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=I\hbox{ identity matrix},

(J.15)

the matrix equality being possible after the choice of a unique basis at
{t_{0}}
and at
t
.
And (simplified notation)

{\widetilde{S^{t_{0}}_{t}}}(P,{\vec{Z}}_{P}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{S^{t_{0}}_{t}}({\vec{Z}}_{P})
.
Then the deformation tensor
{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}
at
P
can be defined by

{{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}}(P).{\vec{Z}}(P)={({S^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{-1}({F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P).{\vec{Z}}(P))+{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)^{T}.({S^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P).{\vec{Z}}(P))\over 2}-{\vec{Z}}(P),

(J.16)

in short:
{\underline{\underline{\varepsilon}}}.{\vec{Z}}={({S^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{-1}(F.{\vec{Z}})+F^{T}.({S^{t_{0}}_{t}}.{\vec{Z}})\over 2}-{\vec{Z}})
.

\bullet
In a manifold:

\Omega
is a manifold
(like a surface in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
from which we cannot take off).
Let
T_{P}{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
be the tangent space à
P
(the fiber at
P
), and

T_{p_{t}}{\Omega_{t}}
be the tangent space à
{p_{t}}
(the fiber at
{p_{t}}
).
In general
T_{P}{\Omega_{t_{0}}}\neq T_{p_{t}}{\Omega_{t}}
(e.g. on a sphere “the Earth”).
The bundle (the union of fibers) at
{t_{0}}
is
T{\Omega_{t_{0}}}=\bigcup_{P\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}}(\{P\}\times T_{P}{\Omega_{t_{0}}})
,
and the bundle at
t
is
T{\Omega_{t}}=\bigcup_{{p_{t}}\in{\Omega_{t}}}(\{{p_{t}}\}\times T_{p_{t}}{\Omega_{t}})
.
Then the shifter

{\widetilde{S^{t_{0}}_{t}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr T{\Omega_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow T{\Omega_{t}}\cr(P,{\vec{Z}}_{P})&\rightarrow{\widetilde{S^{t_{0}}_{t}}}(P,{\vec{Z}}_{P})=({p_{t}},{S^{t_{0}}_{t}}({\vec{Z}}_{P})),\crcr}}\,\right.

(J.17)

is defined such that
{\vec{Z}}_{P}\in T_{P}{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
“as little distorted as possible” along a path.
E.g., on a sphere, if the path is a geodesic,
if
\theta_{t_{0}}
is the angle between
{\vec{Z}}_{P}
and the tangent vector to the geodesic at
P
,
then
\theta_{t_{0}}
is also the angle between
{S^{t_{0}}_{t}}({\vec{Z}}_{P})
and the tangent vector to the geodesic at
{p_{t}}
,
and
{S^{t_{0}}_{t}}({\vec{Z}}_{P})
has the same length than
{\vec{Z}}_{P}

(at constant speed in a car you think the geodesic is a straight line, although
{S^{t_{0}}_{t}}({\vec{Z}}_{P})\neq{\vec{Z}}_{P}
: the Earth is not flat).

J.5.2
The displacement vector

(Affine space framework,
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
open set in
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
.)
Let
P\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
,
{\vec{W}}_{P}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
,
{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)\in{\Omega_{t}}
,
and
d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}={F^{t_{0}}_{t}}\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})
. Define

\delta\widetilde{{\vec{\cal U}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\Omega_{t_{0}}}\times{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}&\rightarrow{\Omega_{t}}\times{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})\cr(P,{\vec{Z}}_{P})&\rightarrow\delta\widetilde{{\vec{\cal U}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P,{\vec{Z}}_{P})=({p_{t}},\delta{{\vec{\cal U}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({\vec{Z}}_{P}))\quad\hbox{with}\quad\delta{{\vec{\cal U}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}({\vec{Z}}_{P})=({F^{t_{0}}_{t}}-{S^{t_{0}}_{t}}).{\vec{Z}}_{P}.\cr}}\,\right.

(J.18)

Then
\delta\widetilde{{\vec{\cal U}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}={F^{t_{0}}_{t}}-{S^{t_{0}}_{t}}
is a two-point tensor. And

\displaystyle{{C^{t_{0}}_{t}}=}

\displaystyle{{}({F^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{T}.{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}=(\delta{\cal U}^{t_{0}}_{t}+S^{t_{0}}_{t})^{T}.(\delta{\cal U}^{t_{0}}_{t}+S^{t_{0}}_{t})}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}I+(S^{t_{0}}_{t})^{T}.\delta{\cal U}^{t_{0}}_{t}+(\delta{\cal U}^{t_{0}}_{t})^{T}.S^{t_{0}}_{t}+(\delta{\cal U}^{t_{0}}_{t})^{T}.\delta{\cal U}^{t_{0}}_{t},}

(J.19)

since
(S^{t_{0}}_{t})^{T}.S^{t_{0}}_{t}=I
identity in
T{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
: Indeed,

((S^{t_{0}}_{t})^{T}.S^{t_{0}}_{t}.{\vec{A}},{\vec{B}})_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}=(S^{t_{0}}_{t}.{\vec{A}},S^{t_{0}}_{t}.{\vec{B}})_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}=({\vec{A}},{\vec{B}})_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
,
cf. (
J.14
), for all
{\vec{A}},{\vec{B}}
.
Then the Green–Lagrange tensor is defined on
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
by

E^{t_{0}}_{t}={1\over 2}({C^{t_{0}}_{t}}-I_{t_{0}})={(S^{t_{0}}_{t})^{T}.\delta{\cal U}^{t_{0}}_{t}+S^{t_{0}}_{t}.(\delta{\cal U}^{t_{0}}_{t})^{T}\over 2}+{1\over 2}(\delta{\cal U}^{t_{0}}_{t})^{T}.\delta{\cal U}^{t_{0}}_{t},

(J.20)

to compare with (
G.47
).

Appendix K
Determinants

K.1
Alternating multilinear form

Let
E
be a vector space, and let

{\cal L}(E,...,E;\mathbb{R}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\cal L}(E^{n};\mathbb{R})
be the set of multilinear forms,
i.e.
m\in{\cal L}(E^{n};\mathbb{R})
iff

m(...,{\vec{x}}+\lambda{\vec{y}},...)=m(...,{\vec{x}},...)+\lambda m(...,{\vec{y}},...)

(K.1)

for all
{\vec{x}},{\vec{y}}\in E
and all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and for all “slot”.
In particular,
m(\lambda_{1}{\vec{x}}_{1},...,\lambda_{n}{\vec{x}}_{n})=(\prod_{i=1,...,n}\lambda_{i})\;m({\vec{x}}_{1},...,{\vec{x}}_{n})
,
for all
\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
and all
{\vec{x}}_{1},...,{\vec{x}}_{n}\in E
.

Definition K.1

If
n=1
then a
1
-alternating multilinear function is a linear form, also called a
1
-form.
If
n\geq 2
then
{A\!\ell}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr E^{n}&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr({\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n})&\rightarrow{A\!\ell}({\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n})\cr}}\,\right\}\in{\cal L}(E^{n};\mathbb{R})

is a
n
-alternating multilinear form iff, for all
{\vec{u}},{\vec{v}}\in E
,

{A\!\ell}(...,{\vec{u}},...,{\vec{v}},...)=-{A\!\ell}(...,{\vec{v}},...,{\vec{u}},...),

(K.2)

the other elements being unchanged.
If
n=1
, the set of
1
-forms is
\Omega^{1}(E)=E^{*}
. If
n\geq 2
,
the set of
n
-alternating multilinear forms is

\Omega^{n}(E)=\{m\in{\cal L}(E^{n};\mathbb{R}):m={A\!\ell}\hbox{ is alternating}\}.

(K.3)

If
{A\!\ell},{B\!\ell}\in\Omega^{n}(E)
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}

then
{A\!\ell}+\lambda{B\!\ell}\in\Omega^{n}(E)
thanks to the linearity for each variable.
Thus
\Omega^{n}(E)
is a vector space, sub-space in
({\cal F}(E^{n};\mathbb{R}),+,.)
.

K.2
Leibniz formula

Particular case
\dim E{=}n
. Let
{A\!\ell}\in\Omega^{n}(E)
(a
n
-alternating multilinear form).
Recall (see e.g. Cartan
[
7
]
):

1- A permutation
\sigma:[1,n]_{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow[1,n]_{\mathbb{N}}
is a bijective map (i.e. one-to-one and onto); Let
S_{n}
be the set of permutations of
[1,n]_{\mathbb{N}}
.

2- A transposition
\tau:[1,n]_{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow[1,n]_{\mathbb{N}}
is a permutation that exchanges two elements, that is,

\exists i,j
s.t.
\tau(...,i,...,j,...)=(...,j,...,i,...)
,
the other elements being unchanged.

3- A permutation is a composition of transpositions (theorem left as an exercise, of see Cartan).
And a permutation is even iff the number of transpositions is even,
and a permutation is odd iff the number of transpositions is odd. Based on:
The parity (even or odd) of a permutation is an invariant.

4- The signature
\varepsilon(\sigma)=\pm 1
of a permutation
\sigma
is
+1
if
\sigma
is even,
and is
-1
if
\sigma
is odd.

Proposition K.2
(Leibniz formula)

Let
{A\!\ell}\in\Omega^{n}(E)
.
Let
({\vec{e}}_{i})_{i=1,...,n}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}({\vec{e}}_{i})
be a basis in
E
.
For all vectors
{\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n}\in E
, with
{\vec{v}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}v^{i}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}
for all
j
,

{A\!\ell}({\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n})=c\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\varepsilon(\sigma)\prod_{i=1}^{n}v^{\sigma(i)}_{i}=c\sum_{\tau\in S_{n}}\varepsilon(\tau)\prod_{i=1}^{n}v^{i}_{\tau(i)}\quad\hbox{(with $c:={A\!\ell}({\vec{e}}_{1},...,{\vec{e}}_{n})$)}.

(K.4)

Thus if
c={A\!\ell}({\vec{e}}_{1},...,{\vec{e}}_{n})
is known, then
{A\!\ell}
is known. Thus
\dim(\Omega^{n}(E))=1
.

(Classic not.:
{\vec{v}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}v_{ij}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,

{A\!\ell}({\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n})=c\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\varepsilon(\sigma)\prod_{i=1}^{n}v_{\sigma(i),i}=c\sum_{\tau\in S_{n}}\varepsilon(\tau)\prod_{i=1}^{n}v_{i,\tau(i)}
.)

Proof.
Let
F:={\cal F}([1,n]_{\mathbb{N}};[1,n]_{\mathbb{N}}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}[1,n]_{\mathbb{N}}^{[1,n]_{\mathbb{N}}}

be the set of functions
i:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr[1,n]_{\mathbb{N}}&\rightarrow[1,n]_{\mathbb{N}}\cr k&\rightarrow i_{k}=i(k)\cr}}\,\right\}
.

{A\!\ell}
being multilinear,

{A\!\ell}({\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n})=\sum_{j_{1}=1}^{n}v^{j_{1}}_{1}{A\!\ell}({\vec{e}}_{j_{1}},{\vec{v}}_{2},...,{\vec{v}}_{n})
(“the first column” development).
By recurrence we get

{A\!\ell}({\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n})=\sum_{j_{1},...,j_{n}=1}^{n}v^{j_{1}}_{1}...v^{j_{n}}_{n}{A\!\ell}({\vec{e}}_{j_{1}},...,{\vec{e}}_{j_{n}})=\sum_{j\in F}\prod_{k=1}^{n}v^{j(k)}_{k}{A\!\ell}({\vec{e}}_{j(1)},...,{\vec{e}}_{j(n)})
.

And
{A\!\ell}({\vec{e}}_{i_{1}},...,{\vec{e}}_{i_{n}})\neq 0

iff
i:k\in\{1,...,n\}\rightarrow i(k)=i_{k}\in\{1,...,n\}
is one-to-one (thus bijective).
Thus

{A\!\ell}({\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n})=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}v^{\sigma(i)}_{i}{A\!\ell}({\vec{e}}_{\sigma(1)},...,{\vec{e}}_{\sigma(n)})=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\varepsilon(\sigma)\prod_{i=1}^{n}v^{\sigma(i)}_{i}{A\!\ell}({\vec{e}}_{1},...,{\vec{e}}_{n})
,
which is the first equality in (
K.4
).
Then

\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\varepsilon(\sigma)\prod_{i=1}^{n}v^{\sigma(i)}_{i}=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\varepsilon(\sigma)\prod_{i=1}^{n}v^{\sigma(\sigma^{-1}(i))}_{\sigma^{-1}(i)}

since
\sigma
is bijectif,
thus

\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\varepsilon(\sigma)\prod_{i=1}^{n}v^{\sigma(i)}_{i}=\sum_{\tau\in S_{n}}\varepsilon(\tau^{-1})\prod_{i=1}^{n}v^{i}_{\tau(i)}
, thus the second equality in (
K.4
) since
\varepsilon(\tau)^{-1}=\varepsilon(\tau)
.
(See Cartan
[
7
]
.)

K.3
Determinant of vectors

Definition K.3

({\vec{e}}_{i})_{i=1,...,n}
being a basis in
E
, the alternating multilinear form
\det_{|{\vec{e}}}\in\Omega^{n}(E)
defined by

\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{e}}_{1},...,{\vec{e}}_{n})=1

(K.5)

is called the determinant relative to
({\vec{e}}_{i})
.
And, with prop.
K.2
(here
c=1
),

\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n})=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\varepsilon(\sigma)\prod_{i=1}^{n}v^{\sigma(i)}_{i}=\sum_{\tau\in S_{n}}\varepsilon(\tau)\prod_{i=1}^{n}v^{i}_{\tau(i)}

(K.6)

is called the determinant of the vectors
{\vec{v}}_{i}
relative to
({\vec{e}}_{i})
. And we write

\Omega^{n}(E)={\rm Vect}\{\det_{|{\vec{e}}}\}\quad\hbox{(the 1-D vector space spanned by $\det_{|{\vec{e}}}$)}.

(K.7)

Thus, if
{A\!\ell}\in\Omega^{n}(E)
then

{A\!\ell}={A\!\ell}({\vec{e}}_{1},...,{\vec{e}}_{n})\,\det_{|{\vec{e}}},

(K.8)

thus if
({\vec{b}}_{i})
is another basis then

\exists c\in\mathbb{R},\quad\det_{|{\vec{b}}}=c\det_{|{\vec{e}}},\quad\hbox{with}\quad c=\det_{|{\vec{b}}}({\vec{e}}_{1},...,{\vec{e}}_{n}).

(K.9)

Exercice K.4

Change of measuring unit: If
({\vec{a}}_{i})
is a basis and
{\vec{b}}_{j}=\lambda{\vec{a}}_{j}
for all
j
, prove

\forall j=1,...,n,\quad{\vec{b}}_{j}=\lambda{\vec{a}}_{j}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\det_{|{\vec{a}}}=\lambda^{n}\det_{|{\vec{b}}}

(K.10)

(relation between volumes relative to a change of measuring unit in the Euclidean case).

Answer
.
\displaystyle\det_{|{\vec{a}}}({\vec{b}}_{1},...,{\vec{b}}_{n})=\det_{|{\vec{a}}}(\lambda{\vec{a}}_{1},...,\lambda{\vec{a}}_{n})\mathop{=}^{multi}_{linear}\lambda^{n}\det_{|{\vec{a}}}({\vec{a}}_{1},...,{\vec{a}}_{n})\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdettrans})}\lambda^{n}\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdettrans})}\lambda^{n}\det_{|{\vec{b}}}({\vec{b}}_{1},...,{\vec{b}}_{n})
.

Proposition K.5

\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n})\neq 0
iff
({\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n})
is a basis,
or equivalently,
\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n})=0
iff
{\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n}
are linearly dependent.

Proof.
If one of the
{\vec{v}}_{i}
is
=\vec{0}
then
\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n})=0
(multilinearity),
and if
{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}c_{i}{\vec{v}}_{i}=0
and one of the
c_{i}\neq 0
and then a
{\vec{v}}_{i}
is a linear combination of the others
thus
\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n})=0
(since
\det_{|{\vec{e}}}
is alternate).
Thus
\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n})\neq 0

\Rightarrow
the
{\vec{v}}_{i}
are independent.
And if the
{\vec{v}}_{i}
are independent then

({\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n})
is a basis, thus
\det_{|{\vec{v}}}({\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n})=1\neq 0
,
with
\det_{|{\vec{v}}}=c\det_{|{\vec{e}}}
, thus
\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n})\neq 0
.

Exercice K.6

In
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Let
{\vec{v}}_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{2}v_{1}^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
and
{\vec{v}}_{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{2}v_{2}^{j}{\vec{e}}_{j}
(duality notations). Prove:

\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{v}}_{1},{\vec{v}}_{2})=v_{1}^{1}v_{2}^{2}-v_{1}^{2}v_{2}^{1}.

(K.11)

Answer
. Development relative to the first column (linearity used for the first vector
{\vec{v}}_{1}=v_{1}^{1}{\vec{e}}_{1}+v_{1}^{2}{\vec{e}}_{2}
):

\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{v}}_{1},{\vec{v}}_{2})=\det_{|{\vec{e}}}(v_{1}^{1}{\vec{e}}_{1}+v_{1}^{2}{\vec{e}}_{2},{\vec{v}}_{2})=v_{1}^{1}\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{e}}_{1},{\vec{v}}_{2})+v_{1}^{2}\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{e}}_{2},{\vec{v}}_{2})
.
Thus (linearity used for the second vector
{\vec{v}}_{2}=v_{2}^{1}{\vec{e}}_{1}+v_{2}^{2}{\vec{e}}_{2}
):

\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{v}}_{1},{\vec{v}}_{2})=0+v_{1}^{1}v_{2}^{2}\det({\vec{e}}_{1},{\vec{e}}_{2})+v_{1}^{2}v_{2}^{1}\det({\vec{e}}_{2},{\vec{e}}_{1})+0=v_{1}^{1}v_{2}^{2}-v_{1}^{2}v_{2}^{1}
.

Exercice K.7

In
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, with
{\vec{v}}_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{3}v_{j}^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
, prove:

\det({\vec{v}}_{1},{\vec{v}}_{2},{\vec{v}}_{3})=\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{3}\varepsilon_{ijk}v_{1}^{i}v_{2}^{j}v_{3}^{k},

(K.12)

where
\varepsilon_{ijk}={1\over 2}(j{-}i)(k{-}j)(k{-}i)
, i.e.
\varepsilon_{ijk}=1
if
(i,j,k)=(1,2,3)
,
(3,1,2)
or
(2,3,1)
(even signature),

\varepsilon_{ijk}=-1
if
(i,j,k)=(3,2,1)
,
(1,3,2)
and
(2,1,3)
(odd signature),
and
\varepsilon_{ijk}=0
otherwise.

Answer
. Development relative to the first column (as in exercise
K.6
).

Result

=v^{1}_{1}v^{2}_{2}v^{3}_{3}+v^{1}_{2}v^{2}_{3}v^{3}_{1}+v^{1}_{3}v^{2}_{1}v^{3}_{2}-v^{3}_{1}v^{2}_{2}v^{1}_{3}-v^{3}_{2}v^{2}_{3}v^{1}_{1}-v^{3}_{3}v^{2}_{1}v^{1}_{2}
.

K.4
Determinant of a matrix

Let
M=[M_{ij}]_{i=1,...,n\atop j=1,...,n}
be a
n^{2}
real matrix.
Let
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}=\mathbb{R}\times...\times\mathbb{R}
(Cartesian product
n
-times) with its canonical basis
({\vec{E}}_{i})
.
Let
{\vec{v}}_{j}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
,
{\vec{v}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}M_{ij}{\vec{E}}_{i}
; So

M=\pmatrix{[{\vec{v}}_{1}]_{|{\vec{E}}},...,[{\vec{v}}_{n}]_{|{\vec{E}}}}
.

Definition K.8

The determinant of the matrix
M=\pmatrix{[{\vec{v}}_{1}]_{|{\vec{E}}},...,[{\vec{v}}_{n}]_{|{\vec{E}}}}
is

\det(M):=\det_{|{\vec{E}}}({\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n}).

(K.13)

Proposition K.9

Let
M^{T}
be the transposed matrix, i.e.,
(M^{T})_{ij}=M_{ji}
for all
i,j
. Then

\det(M^{T})=\det(M).

(K.14)

Proof.
\displaystyle\det[M_{ij}]=\det_{\vec{E}}({\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n})\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdettrans2})}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\varepsilon(\sigma)\prod_{i=1}^{n}v^{\sigma(i)}_{i}=\sum_{\tau\in S_{n}}\varepsilon(\tau)\prod_{i=1}^{n}v^{i}_{\tau(i)}=\det[M_{ji}]
.

K.5
Volume

Definition K.10

Let
({\vec{e}}_{i})
be a Euclidean basis. Consider a parallelepiped in
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
which sides are vectors
{\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n}
; Its algebraic volume relative to
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is

\hbox{algebraic volume}=\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n}).

(K.15)

And its volume relative to
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is (non negative)

\hbox{volume}=\bigl|\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n})\bigr|.

(K.16)

E.g., if
n=1
and
{\vec{v}}=v^{1}{\vec{e}}_{1}
, then
\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{v}})=v^{1}
is the algebraic length of
{\vec{v}}
(relative to the unit of measurement given by
{\vec{e}}_{1}
).
And
|\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{v}})|=|v^{1}|
is the length of
{\vec{v}}
(the norm of
{\vec{v}}
).
(The volume function
({\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n})\rightarrow\bigl|\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n})\bigr|
is
not
a multilinear form, because the absolute value function is not linear.)
E.g., if
n=2
or
3
, see exercises
K.6
-
K.7
.

Notation.

Let
({\vec{e}}_{i})
be a Cartesian basis and
(e^{i})=(dx^{i})
be the dual basis. Then, cf. Cartan
[
8
]
,

\det_{|{\vec{e}}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}e^{1}\wedge...\wedge e^{n}=dx^{1}\wedge...\wedge dx^{n}.

(K.17)

And, for integration, the volume element (non negative) uses a Euclidean basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
and is

d\Omega({\vec{x}})=|\det_{|{\vec{e}}}|=|dx^{1}\wedge...\wedge dx^{n}|{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}dx^{1}...dx^{n}.

(K.18)

Thus the volume of a parallelepiped at
{\vec{x}}
which sides are given by
\delta x_{1}{\vec{u}}_{1},...,\delta x_{n}{\vec{u}}_{n}
is

d\Omega({\vec{x}})(\delta x_{1}{\vec{u}}_{1},...,\delta x_{n}{\vec{u}}_{n})=|\delta x_{1}...\delta x_{n}|\,|\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{u}}_{1},...,{\vec{u}}_{n})|
;
Thus the volume of a polygonal domain
\Omega=\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}P_{i}
where
P_{i}
is a parallelepiped which sides are given by
\delta x_{i,1}{\vec{u}}_{i,1},...,\delta x_{i,n}{\vec{u}}_{i,n}
is

|\Omega|=\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{u}}_{i,1},...,{\vec{u}}_{i,n})|\delta x_{i,1}...\delta x_{i,n}.

(K.19)

And thus (Riemann approach), the volume of a regular domain
\Omega
is written

|\Omega|=\int_{\Omega}d\Omega=\int_{{\vec{x}}\in\Omega}|\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{u}}_{i,1},...,{\vec{u}}_{i,n})|\;dx^{1}...dx^{n}.

(K.20)

In particular, since any regular volume
\Omega
can be approximated with cubes as small as wished,

|\Omega|=\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\delta x_{i,1}...\delta x_{i,n}\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{e}}_{1},...,{\vec{e}}_{n})|=\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\delta x_{i,1}...\delta x_{i,n}|
gives

|\Omega|=\int_{\Omega}d\Omega=\int_{{\vec{x}}\in\Omega}\;dx^{1}...dx^{n}.

(K.21)

Exercice K.11

Let
\Psi:{\vec{q}}=(q_{1},...,q_{n})\in[a_{1},b_{1}]\times...\times[a_{n},b_{n}]\rightarrow{\vec{x}}=(x_{1}=\Psi_{1}({\vec{q}}),...,x_{n}=\Psi_{n}({\vec{q}}))\in\Omega
be a parametric description of a domain
\Omega
;
Prove

d\Omega({\vec{x}})=|J_{\Psi}({\vec{q}})|\,dq^{1}...dq^{n}\quad(=|\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{p}}_{1}({\vec{x}}),...,{\vec{p}}_{n}({\vec{x}}))|\,dq^{1}...dq^{n}),

(K.22)

where

({\vec{p}}_{i}(x))=({\partial\Psi\over\partial q_{i}}({\vec{q}}))
is the parametric basis at
{\vec{x}}=\Psi({\vec{q}})
and

J_{\Psi}({\vec{q}})=\det_{|{\vec{e}}}[d\Psi({\vec{q}})]_{|{\vec{e}}}

is the Jacobian matrix of
\Psi
at
{\vec{q}}
. And thus
|\Omega|=\int_{{\vec{q}}}|J_{\Psi}({\vec{q}})|\,dq^{1}...dq^{n}
.

Answer
. Polar coordinates for illustration purpose (immediate generalization): Consider the disk
\Omega
parametrized with the polar coordinate system
\Psi:{\vec{q}}=(\rho,\theta)\in[0,R]\times[0,2\pi]\rightarrow{\vec{x}}=(x=\rho\cos\theta,y=\rho\sin\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}

where a Euclidean basis
({\vec{e}}_{1},{\vec{e}}_{2})
has been used in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
(so
{\vec{x}}=\rho\cos\theta{\vec{e}}_{1}+\rho\sin\theta{\vec{e}}_{2}
).
The associated polar basis at
{\vec{x}}=\Psi({\vec{q}})
is

({\vec{p}}_{1}({\vec{x}})={\partial\Psi\over\partial\rho}(\rho,\theta),{\vec{p}}_{2}({\vec{x}})={\partial\Psi\over\partial\theta}(\rho,\theta))
,
so
[{\vec{p}}_{1}({\vec{x}})]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{\cos\theta\cr\sin\theta}
and

[{\vec{p}}_{2}({\vec{x}})]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{-\rho\sin\theta\cr\rho\cos\theta}
.
Thus
\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{p}}_{1}({\vec{x}}),{\vec{p}}_{2}({\vec{x}}))=\rho
(
>0
here), thus

d\Omega=|\rho|\,d\rho d\theta=\rho\,d\rho d\theta
.
Thus the volume is
|\Omega|=\int_{{\vec{x}}\in\Omega}d\Omega=\int_{\rho=0}^{R}\int_{\theta=0}^{2\pi}\rho\,d\rho d\theta
(
=\pi R^{2}
).

Exercice K.12

What is the “volume element” on a regular surface
\Sigma
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, called the “surface element”?

Answer
. Let
({\vec{e}}_{1},{\vec{e}}_{2},{\vec{e}}_{3})
be a Euclidean basis in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
We need a regular parametric description

\Psi:(u,v)\in[a_{1},b_{2}]\times[a_{2},b_{2}]\rightarrow{\vec{x}}=\Psi(u,v)=x_{1}(u,v){\vec{e}}_{1}+...+x_{3}(u,v){\vec{e}}_{3}

of the geometric surface
\Sigma={\rm Im}(\Psi)
.
Thus
\vec{t}_{1}({\vec{x}})={\partial\Psi\over\partial u}(u,v)
and
\vec{t}_{2}({\vec{x}})={\partial\Psi\over\partial v}(u,v)
are tangent vectors
at
\Sigma
at
{\vec{x}}=\Psi(u,v)
. Hence a normal unit vector is
{\vec{n}}({\vec{x}})={\vec{t}_{1}({\vec{x}})\wedge\vec{t}_{2}({\vec{x}})\over||\vec{t}_{1}({\vec{x}})\wedge\vec{t}_{2}({\vec{x}})||}
, and thus

\det_{|{\vec{e}}}(\vec{t}_{1},\vec{t}_{2},{\vec{n}})=||\vec{t}_{1}({\vec{x}})\wedge\vec{t}_{2}({\vec{x}})||
is the area of the parallelogram which sides are given by
\vec{t}_{1}
and
\vec{t}_{2}
(volume with height
1
). Thus the surface element at
{\vec{x}}=\Psi(u,v)
is

d\Sigma({\vec{x}})=||{\partial\Psi\over\partial u}(u,v)\wedge{\partial\Psi\over\partial v}(u,v)||\,dudv
. Thus

|\Sigma|=\int_{{\vec{x}}\in\Sigma}d\Sigma({\vec{x}})=\int_{u=a_{1}}^{b_{1}}\int_{v=a_{2}}^{b_{2}}||{\partial\Psi\over\partial u}(u,v)\wedge{\partial\Psi\over\partial v}(u,v)||\,dudv
.

K.6
Determinant of an endomorphism

K.6.1
Definition and basic properties

Definition K.13

The determinant of an endomorphism
L\in{\cal L}(E;E)
relative to a basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is

\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(L):=\det_{|{\vec{e}}}(L.{\vec{e}}_{1},...,L.{\vec{e}}_{n}).

(K.23)

This define
\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}:{\cal L}(E;E)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.
(If the context is not ambiguous, then
\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\det_{|{\vec{e}}}
.)

Proposition K.14

Let
L\in{\cal L}(E;E)
.

1- If
L=I
the identity, then
\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(I)=1
for all basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
.

2- For all
{\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n}\in E
,

\det_{|{\vec{e}}}(L.{\vec{v}}_{1},...,L.{\vec{v}}_{n})=\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(L)\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n}).

(K.24)

3- If
L.{\vec{e}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}L_{ij}{\vec{e}}_{i}
, i.e.
[L]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[L_{ij}]
, then

\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(L)=\det([L]_{|{\vec{e}}})=\det([L_{ij}]).

(K.25)

4- For all
M\in{\cal L}(E;E)
, and with
M\circ L{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}M.L
(thanks to linearity),

\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(M.L)=\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(M)\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(L)=\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(L.M).

(K.26)

5-
L
is invertible iff
\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(L)\neq 0
.

6- If
L
is invertible then

\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(L^{-1})={1\over\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(L)}.

(K.27)

7- If
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
is an inner dot product in
E
and
L^{T}_{g}
is the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
transposed of
L

(i.e.,
(L^{T}_{g}{\vec{w}},{\vec{u}})_{g}=({\vec{w}},L.{\vec{u}})_{g}
for all
{\vec{u}},{\vec{w}}\in E
) then

\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(L^{T}_{g})=\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(L).

(K.28)

8- If
({\vec{e}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
are two
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-orthonormal bases in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
(e.g. two Euclidean basis for the same measuring unit), then
\det_{|{\vec{b}}}=\pm\det_{|{\vec{e}}}
.

Proof.
1-
\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(I)\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdefdetL})}\det_{|{\vec{e}}}(I.{\vec{e}}_{1},...,I.{\vec{e}}_{n})\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdettrans})}\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{e}}_{1},...,{\vec{e}}_{n})=1
, true for all basis.

2- Let
m:({\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n})\rightarrow m({\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n}):=\det_{|{\vec{e}}}(L.{\vec{v}}_{1},...,L.{\vec{v}}_{n})
:
It is a multilinear alternated form, since
L
is linear;
Thus
m\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqald})}m({\vec{e}}_{1},...,{\vec{e}}_{n})\det_{|{\vec{e}}}
;
With
m({\vec{e}}_{1},...,{\vec{e}}_{n})\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdefdetL})}\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(L)
,
thus (
K.24
).

3- Apply (
K.13
) with
M=[L]_{|{\vec{e}}}
to get (
K.25
).

4-

\det_{|{\vec{e}}}((M.L).{\vec{e}}_{1},...,(M.L).{\vec{e}}_{n})=\det_{|{\vec{e}}}(M.(L.{\vec{e}}_{1}),...,M.(L.{\vec{e}}_{n}))\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdetLv})}\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(M)\det_{|{\vec{e}}}(L.{\vec{e}}_{1},...,L.{\vec{e}}_{n})
.

5- If
L
is invertible, then

1=\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(I)=\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(L.L^{-1})=\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(L)\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(L^{-1})
,
thus
\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(L)\neq 0
.

If
\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(L)\neq 0
then

\det_{|{\vec{e}}}(L.{\vec{e}}_{1},...,L.{\vec{e}}_{n})\neq 0
,
thus
(L.{\vec{e}}_{1},...,L.{\vec{e}}_{n})
is a basis,
thus
L
is invertible.

6- (
K.26
) gives

1=\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(I)=\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(L^{-1}.L)=\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(L).\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(L^{-1})
,
thus (
K.27
).

7-
(L^{T}_{g}{\vec{w}},{\vec{u}})_{g}=({\vec{w}},L.{\vec{u}})_{g}
gives
[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[L^{T}_{g}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=([L]_{|{\vec{e}}})^{T}.[g]_{|{\vec{e}}}
,
thus
\det([g]_{|{\vec{e}}})\det([L^{T}_{g}]_{|{\vec{e}}})=\det(([L]_{|{\vec{e}}})^{T})\det([g]_{|{\vec{e}}})
, and
\det([g]_{|{\vec{e}}})\neq 0
(exercise), thus (
K.28
).

8- Let
{\cal P}
be the change of basis endomorphism from
({\vec{e}}_{i})
to
({\vec{b}}_{i})
,
and
P
be the transition matrix from
({\vec{e}}_{i})
to
({\vec{b}}_{i})
.
Both basis being
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-orthonormal,
P^{T}.P=I
, thus
\det(P)=\pm 1=\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}({\cal P})
.
And
\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{b}}_{1},...,{\vec{b}}_{n})=\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\cal P}.{\vec{e}}_{1},...,{\cal P}.{\vec{e}}_{n})=\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}({\cal P})\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({\vec{e}}_{1},...,{\vec{e}}_{n})=\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}({\cal P})\det_{|{\vec{b}}}({\vec{b}}_{1},...,{\vec{b}}_{n})
, thus
\det_{|{\vec{e}}}=\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}({\cal P})\det_{|{\vec{b}}}=\pm\det_{|{\vec{b}}}
.

Definition K.15

Two
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-orthonormal bases
({\vec{e}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
have the same orientation iff
\det_{|{\vec{b}}}=+\det_{|{\vec{e}}}
.

Exercice K.16

Prove
\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(\lambda L)=\lambda^{n}\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(L)
.

Answer
.
\displaystyle\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(\lambda L)=\det_{|{\vec{e}}}(\lambda L.{\vec{e}}_{1},...,\lambda L.{\vec{e}}_{n})=\lambda^{n}\det_{|{\vec{e}}}(L.{\vec{e}}_{1},...,L.{\vec{e}}_{n})=\lambda^{n}\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{e}}}(L)
.

K.6.2
The determinant of an endomorphism is objective

Proposition K.17

Let
({\vec{a}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
be bases in
E
. The determinant of an endomorphism
L\in{\cal L}(E;E)
is objective (observer independent, here basis independent):

(\det([L]_{|{\vec{a}}})=)\quad\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{a}}}(L)=\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{b}}}(L)\quad(=\det([L]_{|{\vec{b}}})).

(K.29)

NB:
But the determinant of
n
vectors is
not
objective, cf. (
K.9
)
(compare the change of basis formula for vectors
[{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}=P^{-1}.[{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}
with the change of basis formula for endomorphisms
[L]_{|{\vec{b}}}=P^{-1}.[L]_{|{\vec{a}}}.P
).

Proof.
Let
({\vec{a}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
be bases in
E
, and
P
be the transition matrix from
({\vec{a}}_{i})
to
({\vec{b}}_{i})
.
The change of basis formula
[L]_{|{\vec{b}}}=P^{-1}.[L]_{|{\vec{a}}}.P
and (
K.26
) give

\det([L]_{|{\vec{b}}})=\det(P^{-1})\det([L]_{|{\vec{a}}})\det(P)=\det([L]_{|{\vec{a}}})
, thus (
K.25
) gives (
K.29
).

Exercice K.18

Let
({\vec{a}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
be bases in
E
,
and
{\cal P}\in{\cal L}(E;E)
be the change of basis endomorphism from
({\vec{a}}_{i})
to
({\vec{b}}_{i})

(i.e.,
{\cal P}.{\vec{a}}_{j}={\vec{b}}_{j}
for all
j
). Prove

\det_{|{\vec{a}}}({\vec{b}}_{1},...,{\vec{b}}_{n})=\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{a}}}({\cal P}),\quad\hbox{thus}\quad\det_{|{\vec{a}}}=\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{a}}}({\cal P})\det_{|{\vec{b}}},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad\det_{|{\vec{b}}}={\det_{|{\vec{a}}}\over\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{a}}}({\cal P})},

(K.30)

Answer
.
\displaystyle\det_{|{\vec{a}}}({\vec{b}}_{1},...,{\vec{b}}_{n})=\det_{|{\vec{a}}}({\cal P}.{\vec{a}}_{1},...,{\cal P}.{\vec{a}}_{n})\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdetLv})}\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{a}}}({\cal P})\det_{|{\vec{a}}}({\vec{a}}_{1},...,{\vec{a}}_{n})=\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{a}}}({\cal P})\,1=\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{a}}}({\cal P})\det_{|{\vec{b}}}({\vec{b}}_{1},...,{\vec{b}}_{n})
, thus (
K.30
) and
\det_{|{\vec{a}}}=\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{a}}}({\cal P})\det_{|{\vec{b}}}
and
\det_{|{\vec{a}}}({\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n})=\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{a}}}({\cal P})\det_{|{\vec{b}}}({\vec{v}}_{1},...,{\vec{v}}_{n})
.

K.7
Determinant of a linear map

(Needed for the deformation gradient
{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P):{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
.)

Let
A
and
B
be vector spaces,
\dim A=\dim B=n
,
and
({\vec{a}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
be bases in
A
and
B
.

K.7.1
Definition and first properties

Definition K.19

The determinant of a linear map
L\in{\cal L}(A;B)
relative to the bases
({\vec{a}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
is

\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}(L):=\det_{|{\vec{b}}}(L.{\vec{a}}_{1},...,L.{\vec{a}}_{n}).

(K.31)

(And
\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}(L){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\det(L)
if the bases are implicit.)

Thus, (
K.13
) gives, with
L.{\vec{a}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}L_{ij}{\vec{b}}_{i}
, i.e.
[L]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}=[L_{ij}]
:

\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}(L)=\det([L]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}})=\det([L_{ij}]).

(K.32)

Proposition K.20

Let
{\vec{u}}_{1},...,{\vec{u}}_{n}\in A
.
Then

\det_{|{\vec{b}}}(L.{\vec{u}}_{1},...,L.{\vec{u}}_{n})=\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}(L)\,\det_{|{\vec{a}}}({\vec{u}}_{1},...,{\vec{u}}_{n}).

(K.33)

Proof.
m:({\vec{u}}_{1},...,{\vec{u}}_{n})\in A^{n}\rightarrow m({\vec{u}}_{1},...,{\vec{u}}_{n}):=\det_{|{\vec{b}}}(L.{\vec{u}}_{1},...,L.{\vec{u}}_{n})\in\mathbb{R}

is a multilinear alternated form, since
L
is linear;
And
m({\vec{a}}_{1},...,{\vec{a}}_{n})=\det_{|{\vec{b}}}(L.{\vec{a}}_{1},...,L.{\vec{a}}_{n})\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdetal})}\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}(L)=\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}(L)\det_{|{\vec{a}}}({\vec{a}}_{1},...,{\vec{a}}_{n})
.
Thus
m=\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}(L)\det_{|{\vec{a}}}
, cf. (
K.9
), thus (
K.33
).

Corollary K.21

Let
A,B,C
be vector spaces such that
\dim A=\dim B=\dim C=n
. Let
({\vec{a}}_{i})
,
({\vec{b}}_{i})
,
({\vec{c}}_{i})
be bases in
A,B,C
.
Let
L:A\rightarrow B
and
M:B\rightarrow C
be linear. Then, with
M\circ L{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}M.L
(thanks to linearity),

\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{c}}}(M.L)=\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}(L)\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{b}},{\vec{c}}}(M).

(K.34)

Proof.
\displaystyle\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{c}}}(M.L)=\det_{|{\vec{c}}}(M.L.{\vec{a}}_{1}),...,M.L.{\vec{a}}_{n}))=\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{b}},{\vec{c}}}(M)\det_{|{\vec{b}}}(L.{\vec{a}}_{1},...,L.{\vec{a}}_{n})=\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{b}},{\vec{c}}}(M)\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}(L)
.

K.7.2
Jacobian of a motion, and dilatation

Let
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
be a motion, let
{t_{0}},t\in\mathbb{R}
, let
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
be the associated motion, let
{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}):=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}):{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}

the deformation gradient at
{p_{t_{0}}}\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
relative to
{t_{0}}
and
t
, cf. (
4.1
).
Let
({\vec{E}}_{i})
be a Euclidean basis in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t_{0}}}}
and
({\vec{e}}_{i})
be a Euclidean basis in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t}}}
for all
t\geq{t_{0}}
,
and
[{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})]_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{e}}}=[F_{ij}({p_{t_{0}}})]
, i.e.,
{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{E}}_{j}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}F_{ij}({p_{t_{0}}}){\vec{e}}_{i}
for all
j
.

Definition K.22

The “volume dilatation” at
{p_{t_{0}}}
, relative to the Euclidean bases

({\vec{E}}_{i})
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t_{0}}}}
and
({\vec{e}}_{i})
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t}}}
, is

J_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{e}}}({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})({p_{t_{0}}}):=\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{e}}}({F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}))\quad(=\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{E}}_{1},...,{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{E}}_{n})=\det([F_{ij}({p_{t_{0}}})])),

(K.35)

usually written
J_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{e}}}:=\det([F]_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{e}}})
(or simply
J=\det(F)
when everything is implicit).

So, at
{t_{0}}
at
{p_{t_{0}}}
,

({p_{t_{0}}},{\vec{E}}_{1},...,{\vec{E}}_{n})
is a unit parallelepiped which volume is
1
relative to the unit of measurement chosen in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
,
and, at
t
at
{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})
,
J_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{e}}}({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})({p_{t_{0}}})=\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{E}}_{1},...,{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{E}}_{n})
is the volume of the parallelepiped
({p_{t}},{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{E}}_{1},...,{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{E}}_{n})
relative to the unit of measurement chosen in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
.

Interpretation: With
{t_{2}}>{t_{1}}\geq{t_{0}}
, and
[{\vec{e}}_{i})
is the basis at
{t_{1}}
and
{t_{2}}
:

\bullet
Dilatation if
J_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{e}}}(\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t_{2}})({p_{t_{0}}})>J_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{e}}}(\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}})({p_{t_{0}}})
(volume increase),

\bullet
contraction if
J_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{e}}}(\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t_{2}})({p_{t_{0}}})<J_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{e}}}(\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}})({p_{t_{0}}})
(volume decrease), and

\bullet
incompressibility if
J_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{e}}}(\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t_{2}})({p_{t_{0}}})=J_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{e}}}(\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t_{1}})({p_{t_{0}}})
for all
t
(volume conservation).

In particular, if
({\vec{e}}_{i})=({\vec{E}}_{i})
then
J_{|{\vec{e}},{\vec{e}}}({\Phi_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}}})({p_{t_{0}}})=1
, and if
t>{t_{0}}
, then

\bullet
Dilatation if
J_{|{\vec{e}},{\vec{e}}}({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})({p_{t_{0}}})>1
(volume increase),

\bullet
contraction if
J_{|{\vec{e}},{\vec{e}}}({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})({p_{t_{0}}})<1
(volume decrease), and

\bullet
incompressibility if
J_{|{\vec{e}},{\vec{e}}}({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})({p_{t_{0}}})=1
for all
t
(volume conservation).

Exercice K.23

Let
({\vec{E}}_{i})
be a Euclidean basis in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
, and let
({\vec{a}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
be two Euclidean bases in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
for the same Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
.
Prove:

J_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{a}}}({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P))=\pm J_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{b}}}({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)).

(K.36)

Answer
.
P
being the transition matrix from
({\vec{a}}_{i})
to
({\vec{b}}_{i})
,
\det(P)=\pm 1
here.
And (
4.30
) gives
[F]_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{a}}}=P.[F]_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{b}}}
,
thus
\det([F]_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{a}}})=\pm\det([F]_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{b}}})
, thus
\det_{|{\vec{a}}}(F.{\vec{E}}_{1},...,F.{\vec{E}}_{n})=\pm\det_{|{\vec{b}}}(F.{\vec{E}}_{1},...,F.{\vec{E}}_{n})
.

K.7.3
Determinant of the transposed

Let
(A,(\cdot,\cdot)_{g})
and
(B,(\cdot,\cdot)_{h})
be finite dimensional Hilbert spaces. Let
L\in{\cal L}(A;B)
(a linear map).
Recall: The transposed
L_{gh}^{T}\in{\cal L}(B;A)
is defined by, for all
{\vec{u}}\in A
and all
{\vec{w}}\in B
, cf. (
A.68
)

(L_{gh}^{T}.{\vec{w}},{\vec{u}})_{g}:=({\vec{w}},L.{\vec{u}})_{h}.

(K.37)

Let
({\vec{a}}_{i})
be a basis in
A
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
be a basis in
B
.
Then

\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}([L^{T}_{gh}]_{|{\vec{b}},{\vec{a}}})=\det([L]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}){\det([(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}]_{|{\vec{a}}})\over\det([(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}]_{|{\vec{b}}})}.

(K.38)

Indeed, (
K.37
) gives

[(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}]_{|{\vec{a}}}.[L_{gh}^{T}]_{|{\vec{b}},{\vec{a}}}=([L]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}})^{T}.[(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}]_{|{\vec{b}}}
.

K.8
Dilatation rate

A unique Euclidean basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
at all time is chosen, and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
is the associated inner dot product.

K.8.1
{\partial{J^{t_{0}}}\over\partial t}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})={J^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})\,{\rm div}{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}})

A regular motion
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
is considered, cf. (
1.5
), and
the Eulerian velocity is
{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}})={\partial{\widetilde{\Phi}}\over\partial t}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
at
{p_{t}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
.
Let
{t_{0}}
be given; The associated motion
{\Phi^{t_{0}}}
is given by

{\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{p_{t}}
when
{p_{t_{0}}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}({t_{0}},{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
,
(
3.1
), and is supposed to be at least
C^{2}
;
The Lagrangian velocity is
{\vec{V}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})={\partial{\Phi^{t_{0}}}\over\partial t}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})
,
and the Eulerian velocity satisfies
{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}})={\partial{\Phi^{t_{0}}}\over\partial t}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})
when
{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})
, cf. (
3.25
). Let
{F^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})={F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})
,
and consider the Jacobian

{J^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})=\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}}))={J^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}}),

(K.39)

Lemma K.24

{\partial{J^{t_{0}}}\over\partial t}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})
satisfies, with
{p_{t}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})
,

{\partial{J^{t_{0}}}\over\partial t}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})={J^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})\,{\rm div}{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}})

(K.40)

(value to be considered at
t
at
{p_{t}}
).
In particular,
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
is incompressible iff
{\rm div}{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}})=0
.

Proof.
Let
{\cal O}
be a origin in
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
.
Let
\overrightarrow{{\cal O}{\Phi^{t_{0}}}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\Phi^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,
{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}V^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,
{\vec{v}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}v^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,

{F^{t_{0}}}.{\vec{E}}_{j}=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}}.{\vec{E}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\partial\Phi^{i}\over\partial X^{j}}{\vec{e}}_{i}
.
Let
[{F^{t_{0}}}]_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{e}}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}F
,
{J^{t_{0}}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}J
and

[d\Phi^{i}]_{|{\vec{E}}}=\pmatrix{{\partial\Phi^{i}\over\partial X^{1}}&...&{\partial\Phi^{i}\over\partial X^{n}}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}d\Phi^{i}
(row matrix).
Thus
J=\det F=\det\pmatrix{d\Phi^{1}\cr\vdots\cr d\Phi^{n}}
, thus (a determinant is multilinear)

{\partial J\over\partial t}=\det\pmatrix{\displaystyle{\partial(d\Phi^{1})\over\partial t}\cr d\Phi^{2}\cr\vdots\cr d\Phi^{n}}+\ldots+\det\pmatrix{d\Phi^{1}\cr\vdots\cr d\Phi^{n-1}\cr\displaystyle{\partial(d\Phi^{n})\over\partial t})}.

With
{\Phi^{t_{0}}}

C^{2}
, thus

\displaystyle{\partial(d\Phi^{i})\over\partial t}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize Swhartz}}d({\partial\Phi^{i}\over\partial t})(t,{p_{t_{0}}})=dV^{i}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})=dv^{i}(t,{p_{t}}).F(t,{p_{t_{0}}})
, cf. (
3.27
).

Thus

\det\pmatrix{\displaystyle{\partial(d\Phi^{1})\over\partial t}\cr d\Phi^{2}\cr\vdots\cr d\Phi^{n}}=\det\pmatrix{\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\partial v^{1}\over\partial x^{i}}d\Phi^{i}\cr d\Phi^{2}\cr\vdots\cr d\Phi^{n}}\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize$\det$ is}}_{\hbox{\footnotesize alternating}}\det\pmatrix{\displaystyle{\partial v^{1}\over\partial x^{1}}d\Phi^{1}\cr d\Phi^{2}\cr\vdots\cr d\Phi^{n}}={\partial v^{1}\over\partial x^{1}}\det\pmatrix{d\Phi^{1}\cr d\Phi^{2}\cr\vdots\cr d\Phi^{n}}={\partial v^{1}\over\partial x^{1}}J

Idem for the other terms, thus

{\partial J\over\partial t}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})={\partial v^{1}\over\partial x^{1}}(t,{p_{t}})\;J(t,{p_{t_{0}}})+\ldots+{\partial v^{n}\over\partial x^{n}}(t,{p_{t}})\;J(t,{p_{t_{0}}})={\rm div}{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}})\;J(t,{p_{t_{0}}}),

i.e. (
K.40
).

Definition K.25

{\rm div}{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}})
is the dilatation rate.

K.8.2
Leibniz formula

Proposition K.26
(Leibniz formula)

Under regularity assumptions (e.g. hypotheses of the Lebesgue theorem to be able to derive under
\int
) we have

\displaystyle{{d\over dt}\Bigl(\int_{{p_{t}}\in{\Omega_{t}}}f(t,{p_{t}})\,d\Omega_{t}\Bigr)=}

\displaystyle{{}\int_{{p_{t}}\in{\Omega_{t}}}\bigl({Df\over Dt}+f\,{\rm div}{\vec{v}}\bigr)(t,{p_{t}})\,d\Omega_{t}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}\int_{{p_{t}}\in{\Omega_{t}}}\bigl({\partial f\over\partial t}+df.{\vec{v}}+f\,{\rm div}({\vec{v}})\bigr)(t,{p_{t}})\,d\Omega_{t}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}\int_{{p_{t}}\in{\Omega_{t}}}\bigl({\partial f\over\partial t}+{\rm div}(f{\vec{v}})\bigr)(t,{p_{t}})\,d\Omega_{t}.}

(K.41)

Proof.
Let

\displaystyle{Z(t):=\int_{p\in{\Omega_{t}}}f(t,p)\,d{\Omega_{t}}=}

\displaystyle{{}\int_{P\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}}f(t,{\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,P))\,{J^{t_{0}}}(t,P)\,d{\Omega_{t_{0}}}.}

(The Jacobian is positive for a regular motion.) Then (derivation under
\int
)

\displaystyle{Z^{\prime}(t)=}

\displaystyle{{}\int_{P\in\Omega_{t_{0}}}{Df\over Dt}(t,{p_{t}})\,{J^{t_{0}}}(t,P)+f(t,{p_{t}}){\partial{J^{t_{0}}}\over\partial t}(t,P)\,d\Omega_{t_{0}}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}\int_{P\in\Omega_{t_{0}}}({Df\over Dt}(t,{p_{t}})\ +f(t,{p_{t}})\,{\rm div}{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}})){J^{t_{0}}}(t,P)\,d\Omega_{t_{0}},}

thanks to (
K.40
).
And
{\rm div}(f{\vec{v}})=df.{\vec{v}}+f\,{\rm div}{\vec{v}}
gives (
K.41
).

Corollary K.27

With
({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})_{g}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{u}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{\vec{w}}
(in the given Euclidean framework),

{d\over dt}\int_{\Omega_{t}}f(t,{p_{t}})\,d\Omega_{t}=\int_{\Omega_{t}}{\partial f\over\partial t}(t,{p_{t}})\,d\Omega_{t}+\int_{\partial\Omega_{t}}(f{\vec{v}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{\vec{n}})(t,{p_{t}})\,d\Gamma_{t},

(K.42)

sum of the temporal variation within
\Omega_{t}
and the flux through the surface
\partial\Omega_{t}
.

Proof.
Apply (
K.41
)
3
.

K.9
\partial J/\partial F=J\,F^{-T}

K.9.1
Meaning of
{\partial\det\over\partial M_{ij}}
?

Let
{\cal M}_{nn}=\{M=[M_{ij}]\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}}\}
be the set of
n*n
matrices, and consider the function

Z:=\det:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\cal M}_{nn}&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr M=[M_{ij}]&\rightarrow Z(M):=\det(M)=\det([M_{ij}]).\cr}}\,\right.

(K.43)

Question: What does
{\partial Z\over\partial M_{ij}}(M)
mean?

Answer: It is the “standard meaning” of a directional derivative
{\partial f\over\partial x_{i}}({\vec{x}})=df({\vec{x}}).{\vec{e}}_{i}
… where here
f=Z
, thus
{\vec{x}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}M
is a matrix (a vector in
{\cal M}_{nn}
), and
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is the canonical basis
(m_{ij})
in
{\cal M}_{nn}
(all the elements of the matrix
m_{ij}
vanish but the element at intersection of line
i
and column
j
which equals
1
). So:

{\partial Z\over\partial M_{ij}}(M):=dZ(M).m_{ij}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{Z(M+hm_{ij})-Z(M)\over h}\;\;(\in\mathbb{R}).

(K.44)

K.9.2
Calculation of
{\partial\det\over\partial M_{ij}}

Proposition K.28

\forall i,j,\;\;{\partial Z\over\partial M_{ij}}(M)=Z(M)\,(M^{-T})_{ij},\quad\hbox{written}\quad{\partial Z\over\partial M}=Z\,M^{-T}.

(K.45)

Proof.
{\partial Z\over\partial M_{ij}}(M):=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{\det(M+hm_{ij})-\det(M)\over h}
;
The development of the determinant
\det(M+hm_{ij})
relative to the column
j
gives

\det(M+h[m_{ij}])=\det(M)+h\,c_{ij}

(K.46)

where
c_{ij}
is the
(i,j)
-th cofactor of
M
;
Thus
{\partial Z\over\partial M_{ij}}(M)=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{Z(M+hm_{ij})-Z(M)\over h}=c_{ij}
;
And since
M^{-1}={1\over\det(M)}[c_{ij}]^{T}
, i.e.
[c_{ij}]=\det(M)M^{-T}
, we get

{\partial Z\over\partial M_{ij}}(M)=\det(M)(M^{-T})_{ij}
, i.e. (
K.45
).

K.9.3
\partial J/\partial F=J\,F^{-T}
usually written
[{\partial J\over\partial F_{ij}}]=J\,F^{-T}

Setting of §
K.8
: With
F:=d\Phi({p_{t_{0}}})
we have

F.{\vec{E}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}F_{ij}{\vec{e}}_{i}
where
F_{ij}={\partial\Phi_{i}\over\partial X_{j}}({p_{t_{0}}})
, and

J_{\Phi,{p_{t_{0}}},{\vec{E}},{\vec{e}}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}J:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr F&\rightarrow J(F):=\det([F_{ij}])\quad(=\det([{\partial\Phi_{i}\over\partial X_{j}}({p_{t_{0}}})]),\crcr}}\,\right.

(K.47)

so,
J(F)
is the Jacobian
\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{e}}}(d\Phi({p_{t_{0}}}))
of
\Phi
at
{p_{t_{0}}}
relative to
({\vec{E}}_{i})
and
({\vec{e}}_{i})
.
Thus (
K.45
) gives:

Corollary K.29

\forall i,j,\;\;{\partial J\over\partial F_{ij}}(F)=J(F)\,([F]^{-T})_{ij},\quad\hbox{written}\quad{\partial J\over\partial F}=J\,F^{-T}.

(K.48)

K.9.4
Interpretation of
{\partial J\over\partial F_{ij}}
?

The first derivations into play are along the directions
{\vec{E}}_{j}
at
{t_{0}}
: The
F_{ij}={\partial\Phi_{i}\over\partial X_{j}}({p_{t_{0}}}):=d\Phi_{i}({p_{t_{0}}}).{\vec{E}}_{j}
.

Question:
{\partial J\over\partial F_{ij}}
is the usual notation for a directional derivative, cf. §
K.9.1
.
So
{\partial J\over\partial F_{ij}}
is the derivative in which direction?

Answer:
1- “Identify”
F\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})
with the tensor
\widetilde{F}\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n*}_{t}},{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};\mathbb{R})

given by
\widetilde{F}(\ell,{\vec{U}})=\ell.(F.{\vec{U}})
;
So, if
F.{\vec{E}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}F_{ij}{\vec{e}}_{i}
then
\widetilde{F}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}F_{ij}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes\pi_{Ej}
,
relative to a basis
({\vec{E}}_{i})
and its covariant dual basis
(\pi_{Ei})
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
and a basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
and
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
.

2- Define the function

\mathop{\widetilde{\det}}_{{\vec{E}},{\vec{e}}}={\widetilde{J}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n*}_{t}},{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};\mathbb{R})&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr\widetilde{F}&\rightarrow{\widetilde{J}}(\widetilde{F}):=J(F)=\det_{{\vec{E}},{\vec{e}}}(F)=\det([F_{ij}])\cr}}\,\right\}
;

3- Then it is meaningful to differentiate
{\widetilde{J}}
along the direction
{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes\pi_{Ej}\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n*}_{t}},{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};\mathbb{R})
to get

{\partial{\widetilde{J}}\over\partial F_{ij}}(\widetilde{F}):=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{{\widetilde{J}}_{|{\vec{e}},{\vec{E}}}(\widetilde{F}+h{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes\pi_{Ej})-{\widetilde{J}}_{|{\vec{e}},{\vec{E}}}(\widetilde{F})\over h}\quad({\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\partial J\over\partial F_{ij}}(F));

(K.49)

This is a derivation in both directions
\pi_{Ej}
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
(past at
{p_{t_{0}}}
)
and

{\vec{e}}_{i}
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
(present at
{p_{t}}
).
What does this derivative mean?
(The answer is unknown to the author.)

Appendix L
Transport of volumes and areas

Here
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}={\mathbb{R}^{3}}
the usual affine space.
Let
{t_{0}},t\in\mathbb{R}
, and
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}:\mathbb{R}\times{\Omega_{t_{0}}}\rightarrow{\Omega_{t}}
, see (
3.1
).
Let
F_{P}=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
.
Let
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
be a Euclidean dot product in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
(English, French…),
with
||.||_{g}
the associated norm.

L.1
Transformed parallelepiped

The Jacobian of
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
at
P
relative to a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Euclidean bases is defined in (
K.35
): With
F_{P}={F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
,

J_{P}=J(P):=\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P).{\vec{E}}_{1},...,{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P).{\vec{E}}_{n})),\quad\hbox{and}\quad J_{P}>0

(L.1)

the motion being supposed regular.
Thus, if
({{\vec{U}}_{1P}},...,{{\vec{U}}_{nP}})
is a parallelepiped at
P
at
{t_{0}}
, if
{\vec{u}}_{ip}=F_{P}.{\vec{U}}_{iP}
,
then
({{\vec{u}}_{1p}},...,{{\vec{u}}_{np}})
is a parallelepiped at
p
at
t
which volume is

\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({{\vec{u}}_{1p}},...,{{\vec{u}}_{np}})=J_{P}\;\det_{|{\vec{e}}}({{\vec{U}}_{1P}},...,{{\vec{U}}_{nP}}).

(L.2)

L.2
Transformed volumes

Riemann integrals and (
L.2
) give the change of variable formula: For any regular function
f:{\Omega_{t}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,

\int_{{p_{t}}\in{\Omega_{t}}}f({p_{t}})\,d\Omega_{t}=\int_{P\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}}f({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P))\,|J(P)|\,d\Omega_{t_{0}}.

(L.3)

(See (
K.18
):
d{\Omega_{t}}
is a positive measure: It is not a multilinear form.)
In particular,

|{\Omega_{t}}|=\int_{{p_{t}}\in{\Omega_{t}}}d{\Omega_{t}}({p_{t}})=\int_{P\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}}|J(P)|\,d{\Omega_{t_{0}}}(P).

(L.4)

(With
J(P)>0
for regular motions.)

L.3
Transformed parallelogram

Consider two independent vectors
{{\vec{U}}_{1P}},{{\vec{U}}_{2P}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
at
{t_{0}}
at
P
,
and the vectors
{{\vec{u}}_{1p}}=F_{P}.{{\vec{U}}_{1P}}
and
{{\vec{u}}_{2p}}=F_{P}.{{\vec{U}}_{2P}}
at
t
at
p={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
. Since
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
is a diffeomorphism,
{{\vec{u}}_{1p}}
and
{{\vec{u}}_{2p}}
are independent.

Then choose a Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
(English, French…) to be able to use the vectorial product, cf. (
E.15
), the same at all time
t
. Then the areas of the parallelograms are

||{{\vec{U}}_{1P}}\wedge{{\vec{U}}_{2P}}||_{g}\quad\hbox{and}\quad||{{\vec{u}}_{1p}}\wedge{{\vec{u}}_{2p}})||_{g},

(L.5)

and unit normal vectors to the quadrilaterals are

{{\vec{N}}_{P}}={{{\vec{U}}_{1P}}\wedge{{\vec{U}}_{2P}}\over||{{\vec{U}}_{1P}}\wedge{{\vec{U}}_{2P}}||_{g}}\;\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}},\quad\hbox{and}\quad{{\vec{n}}_{p}}={{{\vec{u}}_{1p}}\wedge{{\vec{u}}_{2p}}\over||{{\vec{u}}_{1p}}\wedge{{\vec{u}}_{2p}}||_{g}}\;\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}.

(L.6)

Proposition L.1

If
{{\vec{u}}_{1p}}=F_{P}.{{\vec{U}}_{1P}}
and
{{\vec{u}}_{2p}}=F_{P}.{{\vec{U}}_{2P}}
, then

{{\vec{u}}_{1p}}\wedge{{\vec{u}}_{2p}}=J_{P}\,F_{P}^{-T}.\bigl({{\vec{U}}_{1P}}\wedge{{\vec{U}}_{2P}}\bigr),\quad\hbox{and}\quad||{{\vec{u}}_{1p}}\wedge{{\vec{u}}_{2p}}||_{g}=J_{P}\,||F_{P}^{-T}.({{\vec{U}}_{1P}}\wedge{{\vec{U}}_{2P}})||_{g},

(L.7)

since
J_{P}>0
(for regular motions), and

{{\vec{n}}_{p}}={F_{P}^{-T}.{{\vec{N}}_{P}}\over||F_{P}^{-T}.{{\vec{N}}_{P}}||_{g}}\quad(\neq F_{P}.{{\vec{N}}_{P}}\hbox{ in general}).

(L.8)

Proof.
Let
{{\vec{W}}_{P}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t_{0}}}}
and
{{\vec{w}}_{p}}=F_{P}.{{\vec{W}}_{P}}
.
Then the volume of the parallelepiped
({{\vec{u}}_{1p}},{{\vec{u}}_{2p}},{{\vec{w}}_{p}})
is

\displaystyle{({{\vec{u}}_{1p}}\wedge{{\vec{u}}_{2p}},{{\vec{w}}_{p}})_{g}=}

\displaystyle{{}\det({{\vec{u}}_{1p}},{{\vec{u}}_{2p}},{{\vec{w}}_{p}})=\det(F_{P}.{{\vec{U}}_{1P}},F_{P}.{{\vec{U}}_{2P}},F_{P}.{{\vec{W}}_{P}})=\det(F_{P})\det({{\vec{U}}_{1P}},{{\vec{U}}_{2P}},{{\vec{W}}_{P}})}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}J_{P}\,({{\vec{U}}_{1P}}\wedge{{\vec{U}}_{2P}},{{\vec{W}}_{P}})_{g}=J_{P}\,({{\vec{U}}_{1P}}\wedge{{\vec{U}}_{2P}},F_{P}^{-1}.{{\vec{w}}_{p}})_{g}=J_{P}\,(F_{P}^{-T}.({{\vec{U}}_{1P}}\wedge{{\vec{U}}_{2P}}),{{\vec{w}}_{p}})_{g},}

(L.9)

for all
{{\vec{w}}_{p}}
, thus (
L.7
),
thus
{{{\vec{u}}_{1p}}\wedge{{\vec{u}}_{2p}}\over||{{\vec{u}}_{1p}}\wedge{{\vec{u}}_{2p}}||_{g}}={J_{P}\,F_{P}^{-T}.({{\vec{U}}_{1P}}\wedge{{\vec{U}}_{2P}})\over J_{P}||F_{P}^{-T}.({{\vec{U}}_{1P}}\wedge{{\vec{U}}_{2P}})||_{g}},

thus (
L.8
).

L.4
Transformed surface

L.4.1
Deformation of a surface

A parametrized surface
{\Psi_{t_{0}}}
in
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
and the associated geometric surface
S_{t_{0}}
are defined by

{\Psi_{t_{0}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr[a,b]\times[c,d]&\rightarrow{\Omega_{t_{0}}}\cr(u,v)&\rightarrow P={\Psi_{t_{0}}}(u,v)\crcr}}\,\right\}\quad\hbox{and}\quad S_{t_{0}}={\rm Im}({\Psi_{t_{0}}})\subset{\Omega_{t_{0}}}.

(L.10)

(It is also represented after a choice of an origin
O
by the vector valued parametrized surface

{{\vec{r}}_{t_{0}}}=\overrightarrow{O\Psi_{t_{0}}}
.)

The transformed parametric surface is
{\Psi_{t}}:={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}\circ{\Psi_{t_{0}}}
and the associated geometric surface is
S_{t}
:

{\Psi_{t}}:={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}\circ{\Psi_{t_{0}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr[a,b]\times[c,d]&\rightarrow{\Omega_{t_{0}}}\cr(u,v)&\rightarrow p={\Psi_{t}}(u,v)={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}({\Psi_{t_{0}}}(u,v))={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)\crcr}}\,\right\}\quad\hbox{and}\quad S_{t}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(S_{t_{0}}).

(L.11)

(After a choice of an origin
O
, the associated vector valued parametrized surface is
{\vec{r}}_{t}=\overrightarrow{O\Psi_{t}}
.)

Let
({\vec{E}}_{1},{\vec{E}}_{2})
be the canonical basis in the space
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\supset[a,b]\times[c,d]=\{(u,v)\}
of parameters.

The surface
{\Psi_{t_{0}}}
is supposed to be regular, that is,
{\Psi_{t_{0}}}
is
C^{1}
and,
for all
P={\Psi_{t_{0}}}(u,v)\in{S_{t_{0}}}
, the tangents vectors

{\vec{T}_{1P}}
and
{\vec{T}_{2P}}
at
P
are independent, that is,

\left.\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&{\vec{T}_{1P}}:=d{\Psi_{t_{0}}}(u,v).{\vec{E}}_{1}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\partial{\Psi_{t_{0}}}\over\partial u}(u,v),\cr&{\vec{T}_{2P}}:=d{\Psi_{t_{0}}}(u,v).{\vec{E}}_{2}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\partial{\Psi_{t_{0}}}\over\partial v}(u,v),\crcr}}\,\right\}\quad\hbox{and}\quad{\vec{T}_{1P}}\wedge{\vec{T}_{2P}}\neq\vec{0}.

(L.12)

And the tangent vectors at
S_{t}
at
p={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
at
t
are

\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&{\vec{t}_{1p}}:=d\Psi_{t}(u,v).{\vec{E}}_{1}={\partial\Psi_{t}\over\partial u}(u,v),\quad\hbox{so}\quad{\vec{t}_{1p}}=F_{P}.{\vec{T}_{1P}}\quad(=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P).{\partial{\Psi_{t_{0}}}\over\partial u}(u,v)),\cr&{\vec{t}_{2p}}:=d\Psi_{t}(u,v).{\vec{E}}_{2}={\partial\Psi_{t}\over\partial v}(u,v),\quad\hbox{so}\quad{\vec{t}_{2p}}=F_{P}.{\vec{T}_{2P}}\quad(=d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P).{\partial{\Psi_{t_{0}}}\over\partial v}(u,v)).\crcr}}\,\right.

(L.13)

These vectors are independent since
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
is a diffeomorphism and
{\Psi_{t_{0}}}
is regular.
In facts, we used tangent vectors to curves and their push-forwards, cf. figure
4.1
and §
6.5.1
.

L.4.2
Euclidean dot product and unit normal vectors

Then choose a Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
(English, French…), to be able to use the vectorial product, cf. (
E.15
), the same at all time
t
.
Then the scalar area elements
d{\Sigma_{P}}
at
P
at
S_{t_{0}}
relative to
{\Psi_{t_{0}}}
, and

d{\sigma_{p}}
at
p
at
S_{t}
relative to
\Psi_{t}
, are

\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&d{\Sigma_{P}}:=||{\partial{\Psi_{t_{0}}}\over\partial u}(u,v)\wedge{\partial{\Psi_{t_{0}}}\over\partial v}(u,v)||_{g}\,du\,dv\quad(=||{\vec{T}_{1P}}\wedge{\vec{T}_{2P}}||_{g}\,du\,dv),\cr&d{\sigma_{p}}:=||{\partial\Psi_{t}\over\partial u}(u,v)\wedge{\partial\Psi_{t}\over\partial v}(u,v)||_{g}\,du\,dv\quad(=||{\vec{t}_{1p}}\wedge{\vec{t}_{2p}}||_{g}\,du\,dv).\crcr}}\,\right.

(L.14)

And the areas of
{S_{t_{0}}}
and
S_{t}
are

\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&|S_{t_{0}}|=\int_{P\in{S_{t_{0}}}}d{\Sigma_{P}}:=\int_{u=a}^{b}\int_{v=c}^{d}||{\partial{\Psi_{t_{0}}}\over\partial u}(u,v)\wedge{\partial{\Psi_{t_{0}}}\over\partial v}(u,v)||_{g}\,du\,dv,\cr&|S_{t}|=\int_{p\in S_{t}}d{\sigma_{p}}:=\int_{u=a}^{b}\int_{v=c}^{d}||{\partial\Psi_{t}\over\partial u}(u,v)\wedge{\partial\Psi_{t}\over\partial v}(u,v)||_{g}\,du\,dv.\crcr}}\,\right.

(L.15)

(See (
K.18
):
d{\Sigma_{P}}
and
d{\sigma_{p}}
are positive measures: They are not multilinear forms.)

And the unit normal vectors
{{\vec{N}}_{P}}
at
S_{t_{0}}
at
P
at
{t_{0}}
and
{{\vec{n}}_{p}}
at
S_{t}
at
p
at
t
are

\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&{{\vec{N}}_{P}}={{\partial{\Psi_{t_{0}}}\over\partial u}(u,v)\wedge{\partial{\Psi_{t_{0}}}\over\partial v}(u,v)\over||{\partial{\Psi_{t_{0}}}\over\partial u}(u,v)\wedge{\partial{\Psi_{t_{0}}}\over\partial v}(u,v)||_{g}}\quad(={{\vec{T}_{1P}}\wedge{\vec{T}_{2P}}\over||{\vec{T}_{1P}}\wedge{\vec{T}_{2P}}||_{g}})\cr&{{\vec{n}}_{p}}={{\partial\Psi_{t}\over\partial u}(u,v)\wedge{\partial\Psi_{t}\over\partial v}(u,v)\over||{\partial\Psi_{t}\over\partial u}(u,v)\wedge{\partial\Psi_{t}\over\partial v}(u,v)||_{g}}\quad(={{\vec{t}_{1p}}\wedge{\vec{t}_{2p}}\over||{\vec{t}_{1p}}\wedge{\vec{t}_{2p}}||_{g}}=.\crcr}}\,\right.

(L.16)

Then the vectorial area elements
d{\vec{\Sigma}_{P}}
at
P
at
S_{t_{0}}={\rm Im}({\Psi_{t_{0}}})
relative to
{\vec{r}}_{t_{0}}

and
d{{\vec{\sigma}}_{p}}
at
p
at
S_{t}={\rm Im}(\Psi_{t})
relative to
\Psi_{t}
are

\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&d{\vec{\Sigma}_{P}}:={{\vec{N}}_{P}}\,d{\Sigma_{P}}={\partial{\Psi_{t_{0}}}\over\partial u}(u,v)\wedge{\partial{\Psi_{t_{0}}}\over\partial v}(u,v)\,du\,dv\quad(={\vec{T}_{1P}}\wedge{\vec{T}_{2P}}\,du\,dv)\cr&d{{\vec{\sigma}}_{p}}:={{\vec{n}}_{p}}\,d{\sigma_{p}}={\partial\Psi_{t}\over\partial u}(u,v)\wedge{\partial\Psi_{t}\over\partial v}(u,v)\,du\,dv\quad(={\vec{t}_{1p}}\wedge{\vec{t}_{2p}}\,du\,dv).\cr}}\,\right.

(L.17)

(Useful to get the flux through a surface:
\int_{\Gamma}{\vec{f}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{\vec{n}}\,d\sigma=\int_{\Gamma}{\vec{f}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}d{\vec{\sigma}}
.)

(NB:
d{\vec{\Sigma}_{P}}
and
d{{\vec{\sigma}}_{p}}
are not multilinear since
d{\Sigma_{P}}
and
d{\sigma_{p}}
are not.)

L.4.3
Relations between surfaces

{\vec{t}_{1p}}\wedge{\vec{t}_{2p}}=J_{P}\,F_{P}^{-T}.({\vec{T}_{1P}}\wedge{\vec{T}_{2P}})
, cf. (
L.7
), gives

{\partial\Psi_{t}\over\partial u}(u,v)\wedge{\partial\Psi_{t}\over\partial v}(u,v)=J_{P}\,F_{P}^{-T}.({\partial{\Psi_{t_{0}}}\over\partial u}(u,v)\wedge{\partial{\Psi_{t_{0}}}\over\partial v}(u,v)).

(L.18)

This gives the relation between vectorial and scalar area elements,

{\vec{n}}\,d\sigma_{p}=d{\vec{\sigma}}_{p}=J_{P}\,F_{P}^{-T}.d{\vec{\Sigma}_{P}}=J_{P}\,F_{P}^{-T}.{{\vec{N}}_{P}}\,d{\Sigma_{P}},\quad\hbox{and}\quad d\sigma_{p}=J_{P}\,||F_{P}^{-T}.{{\vec{N}}_{P}}||_{g}\,d{\Sigma_{P}}.

(L.19)

(Check with (
L.8
).)

L.5
Piola identity

Reminder:
Let
M=[M^{i}_{j}]
be a
3*3
matrix function.
We use the usual divergence in continuum mechanics (non objective) given by

{\rm div}M:=\pmatrix{{\partial M^{1}_{1}\over\partial X^{1}}+{\partial M^{1}_{2}\over\partial X^{2}}+{\partial M^{1}_{3}\over\partial X^{3}}\cr{\partial M^{2}_{1}\over\partial X^{1}}+{\partial M^{2}_{2}\over\partial X^{2}}+{\partial M^{2}_{3}\over\partial X^{3}}\cr{\partial M^{3}_{1}\over\partial X^{1}}+{\partial M^{3}_{2}\over\partial X^{2}}+{\partial M^{3}_{3}\over\partial X^{3}}\cr}=\pmatrix{{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}{\partial M^{1}_{j}\over\partial X^{j}}\cr{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}{\partial M^{2}_{j}\over\partial X^{j}}\cr{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}{\partial M^{3}_{j}\over\partial X^{j}}\cr}
, cf. (
S.65
).
And if
{\rm Cof}(M)
is the matrix of cofactors
(in
{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
:
{\rm Cof}(M)^{i}_{j}=M^{i+1}_{j+1}M^{i+2}_{j+2}-M^{i+1}_{j+2}M^{i+2}_{j+1}
),
then

M^{-1}={1\over\det M}{\rm Cof}(M)^{T}
, i.e.,

(\det M)M^{-1}={\rm Cof}(M)^{T}.

(L.20)

The framework being Euclidean, we use a Euclidean basis and the associated matrix, and thus (matrix meaning)

J(P)F(P)^{-T}={\rm Cof}(F(P)){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\rm Cof}(F)(P),\quad\hbox{written}\quad JF^{-T}={\rm Cof}(F).

(L.21)

Proposition L.2
(Piola identity)

In
{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
, we have

{\rm div}(JF^{-T})(P)=0,\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad\forall i,\;{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}{\partial{\rm Cof}(F)^{i}_{j}\over\partial X^{j}}(P)=0.

(L.22)

Also written
{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}{\partial\over\partial X^{j}}(J{\partial X^{i}\over\partial x^{j}})=0
…
NB: (
L.22
) is a just a matrix computation since we used the divergence of a matrix (we used components relative to a given basis).

Proof.
We are in
{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
, thus

{\rm Cof}(F)^{i}_{j}=F^{i+1}_{j+1}F^{i+2}_{j+2}-F^{i+1}_{j+2}F^{i+2}_{j+1}
,
and
F=[d\Phi_{t}]=[{\partial\varphi^{i}\over\partial X^{j}}]
, that is,
F^{i}_{j}={\partial\varphi^{i}\over\partial X^{j}}
. Thus

{\partial{\rm Cof}(F)^{i}_{j}\over\partial X^{j}}={\partial^{2}\varphi^{i+1}\over\partial X^{j}\partial X^{j+1}}{\partial\varphi^{i+2}\over\partial X^{j+2}}+{\partial\varphi^{i+1}\over\partial X^{j+1}}{\partial^{2}\varphi^{i+2}\over\partial X^{j}\partial X^{j+2}}-{\partial^{2}\varphi^{i+1}\over\partial X^{j}\partial X^{j+2}}{\partial\varphi^{i+2}\over\partial X^{j+1}}-{\partial\varphi^{i+1}\over\partial X^{j+2}}{\partial^{2}\varphi^{i+2}\over\partial X^{j}\partial X^{j+1}}.

Thus, for all
i=1,2,3
, we get
{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}{{\partial{\rm Cof}(F)^{i}_{j}\over\partial X^{j}}}=0

(the terms cancel each other out two by two).

L.6
Piola transformation

Let
{\vec{u}}
be a vector field in
{\Omega_{t}}
.
The goal is to find a vector field
{\vec{U}}_{\rm Piola}
in
{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
s.t. for all open subset
{\omega_{t}}\subset{\Omega_{t}}
with
{\omega_{t_{0}}}={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}^{-1}({\omega_{t}})\subset{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
,

\int_{\partial{\omega_{t_{0}}}}{\vec{U}}_{\rm Piola}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{\vec{N}}\,d\Sigma=\int_{\partial{\omega_{t}}}{\vec{u}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{\vec{n}}\,d\sigma,

(L.23)

or

\int_{\omega_{t_{0}}}{\rm div}({\vec{U}}_{\rm Piola})\,d{\Omega_{t_{0}}}=\int_{\omega_{t}}{\rm div}({\vec{u}})\,d{\Omega_{t}},

(L.24)

i.e.

\int_{P\in{\omega_{t_{0}}}}{\rm div}({\vec{U}}_{\rm Piola})(P)\,d{\Omega_{t_{0}}}=\int_{P\in{\omega_{t_{0}}}}{\rm div}({\vec{u}})({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P))\,J(P)\,d{\Omega_{t_{0}}}.

(L.25)

(The motion is supposed to be regular, so
J(P)>0
). Thus we want

{\rm div}{\vec{U}}_{\rm Piola}(P)=J(P)\,{\rm div}{\vec{u}}(p)\quad\hbox{when}\quad p={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P).

(L.26)

Definition L.3

The Piola transform is the map

\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr C^{\infty}(\Omega_{t};{\mathbb{R}^{n}})&\rightarrow C^{\infty}(\Omega_{t_{0}};{\mathbb{R}^{n}})\cr{\vec{u}}&\rightarrow{\vec{U}}_{\rm Piola},\quad\ \framebox{$\displaystyle{\vec{U}}_{\rm Piola}(P):=J(P)F(P)^{-1}.{\vec{u}}(p)$}\quad\hbox{when}\quad p={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P).\crcr}}\,\right.

(L.27)

(So
{\vec{U}}_{\rm Piola}(P)=J(P)\Phi^{*}({\vec{u}})(P)
where
\Phi^{*}({\vec{u}})(P)=F(P)^{-1}.{\vec{u}}(p)=
the pull-back with
\Phi={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
.)

Proposition L.4

With
p={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
,
{\vec{U}}_{\rm Piola}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}U_{\rm Piola}^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
and
{\vec{u}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}u^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
we get

{\rm div}{\vec{U}}_{\rm Piola}(P)=J(P)\,{\rm div}{\vec{u}}(p),\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\partial U_{\rm Piola}^{i}\over\partial X^{i}}(P)=J(P){\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\partial u^{i}\over\partial x^{i}}(p).
