(L.28)

Proof.
{\rm div}({\underline{\underline{\tau}}}.{\vec{w}})\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdtw0})}}{\widetilde{\rm div}}({\underline{\underline{\tau}}}).{\vec{w}}+{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}d{\vec{w}}
gives

{\rm div}((JF^{-1}).({\vec{u}}\circ{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}))(P)=({\rm div}(JF^{-T})(P),{\vec{u}}(p))_{g}+(J(P)F(P)^{-1})\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}(d{\vec{u}}(p).F(P))\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqidpiola})}}0+J(P)(F(P).F(P)^{-1})\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}d{\vec{u}}(p)=J(P)\,I\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}d{\vec{u}}(p)=J(P){\rm div}{\vec{u}}(p)
,
which gives (
L.28
).

Appendix M
Work and power

M.1
Definitions

M.1.1
Work

(Thermodynamic like approach.)
The elementary work is a differential form
\alpha
, e.g.
\alpha=dU
(internal energy density),
\alpha=\delta W=
(elementary work).
Consider a regular curve
{c}:t\in[{t_{0}},T]\rightarrow{c}(t)\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
.
And let
{\vec{v}}(t,{c}(t)):={\vec{c}\,^{\prime}}(t)
.
The work of
\alpha
along the curve is

\displaystyle{\int_{c}\alpha:=}

\displaystyle{{}\int_{t={t_{0}}}^{T}\alpha(t,{c}(t)).{\vec{c}\,^{\prime}}(t)\,dt{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\int_{t={t_{0}}}^{T}\alpha.d{\vec{c}}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}\int_{t={t_{0}}}^{T}\alpha(t,{c}(t)).{\vec{v}}(t,{c}(t))\,dt{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\int_{t={t_{0}}}^{T}\alpha.{\vec{v}}\,dt.}

(M.1)

E.g.,
{W^{t_{0}}_{T}}(\alpha,{c})=\int_{c}\delta W=
work along
c
of the differential form
\alpha=\delta W
.

Then consider an object
{\it O\!b\!j}
and its motion
{\widetilde{\Phi}}:(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})\rightarrow p(t)={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})={\widetilde{\Phi}}_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}(t)\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
, the curves
c_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}:t\in[{t_{0}},T]\rightarrow p(t)={\widetilde{\Phi}}_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}(t)\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
,
and the Eulerian velocities
{\vec{v}}(t,p(t))={\widetilde{\Phi}}_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}{}^{\prime}(t)
.
The work for
{\it O\!b\!j}
and a Eulerian differential form
\alpha
along
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
is the sum of work of
\alpha
of all particles, formally
\sum_{{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}\in{\it O\!b\!j}}(\int_{c_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}}}\alpha_{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
. So with the associated motion

{\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,{p_{t_{0}}})={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})=p(t)={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t)
when
{p_{t_{0}}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}({t_{0}},{P_{\!{\it O\!b\!j}}})
, and with
\Omega_{t}={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{\it O\!b\!j})
,

{W^{t_{0}}_{T}}({\widetilde{\Phi}}):=\int_{{p_{t_{0}}}\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}}\int_{t={t_{0}}}^{T}\alpha(t,{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t)).{\vec{v}}(t,{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{p_{t_{0}}}}(t))\,dt\,d{\Omega_{t_{0}}}=\int_{t={t_{0}}}^{T}\int_{{p_{t}}\in{\Omega_{t}}}\alpha(t,{p_{t}})).{\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}})\,d{\Omega_{t}}\,dt.

(M.2)

(The last equality if Fubini theorem can be applied, e.g. if
\alpha
is
C^{0}
and
{\Phi^{t_{0}}}
is
C^{1}
,
{\it O\!b\!j}
being bounded.)

Exercice M.1

If
\alpha
is a stationary and exact differential form,
\alpha=dU
, then prove that

\int_{c}dU=U({c}(T))-U({c}({t_{0}})){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\Delta U

(M.3)

only depends on the extremities
{c}({t_{0}})
and
{c}(T)
of the curve
c
.

Answer
.
\int_{c}dU=\int_{t={t_{0}}}^{T}dU({c}(t)).{\vec{c}\,^{\prime}}(t)\,dt=\int_{t={t_{0}}}^{T}{d(U\circ{c})\over dt}(t)\,dt=[U\circ{c}]_{t_{0}}^{T}=U({c}(T))-U({c}({t_{0}}))
.

Remark (continuum mechanics): An observer chooses a Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}=.\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,.=.\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}.

(if
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
is imposed and implicit).
And if he chooses to represent a linear form
\alpha_{t}(p_{t})
with its
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Riesz representation vector
{\vec{f}}_{t}(p_{t})
(observer dependent),
cf. (
B.8
), then

\int_{c}\alpha=\int_{t={t_{0}}}^{T}\alpha(t,{c}(t)).{\vec{c}\,^{\prime}}(t)\,dt=\int_{t={t_{0}}}^{T}{\vec{f}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}d{\vec{c}}=\int_{t={t_{0}}}^{T}{\vec{f}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{\vec{v}}\,dt.

(M.4)

M.1.2
And its associated power density

Definition: The power density of a differential form
\alpha

along
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
is the Eulerian function

\psi:=\alpha.{\vec{v}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\hbox{\large$\cal C$}=\bigcup_{t\in[{t_{0}},T]}(\{t\}\times{\Omega_{t}})&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr(t,p)&\rightarrow\psi(t,p)=\alpha(t,p).{\vec{v}}(t,p).\crcr}}\,\right.

(M.5)

And the power at
t
is

{\cal P}_{t}({\widetilde{\Phi}})={\cal P}(t,{\widetilde{\Phi}}):=\int_{p\in{\Omega_{t}}}\psi(t,p)\,d{\Omega_{t}}=\int_{p\in{\Omega_{t}}}\alpha_{t}(p).{\vec{v}}_{t}(p)\,d{\Omega_{t}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\cal P}(t,{\vec{v}}_{t})={\cal P}_{t}({\vec{v}}_{t}).

(M.6)

Remark: With a Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
, then with the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Riesz representation vector
{\vec{f}}
of
\alpha

(observer dependent) we get

\psi={\vec{f}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{\vec{v}},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad\psi(t,p)={\vec{f}}(t,p)\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,{\vec{v}}(t,p)\quad(=({\vec{f}}(t,p),{\vec{v}}(t,p))_{g}),

(M.7)

which gives

{\cal P}(t,{\widetilde{\Phi}}):=\int_{p\in{\Omega_{t}}}{\vec{f}}(t,p)\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{\vec{v}}(t,p)\,d{\Omega_{t}}.

M.2
Piola–Kirchhoff tensors

Consider a regular Eulerian velocity field
{\vec{v}}
, so
d{\vec{v}}
is an endomorphism (identified with a
{1\choose 1}
tensor).
Then we need another endomorphism
{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}
(identified with a
{1\choose 1}
to get the objective double contraction

{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}d{\vec{v}}:={\rm Tr}({\underline{\underline{\tau}}}.d{\vec{v}}),

(M.8)

which means
{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}(t,p)\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}d{\vec{v}}(t,p):={\rm Tr}({\underline{\underline{\tau}}}(t,p).d{\vec{v}}(t,p))
.

Quantification: With a basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
at
t
and

{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}=\sum_{ij}\tau^{i}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
and
d{\vec{v}}=\sum_{jk}v^{j}_{|k}{\vec{e}}_{j}\otimes e^{k}
(the endomorphisms have been written like
{1\choose 1}
tensors for calculation purpose),
{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}.d{\vec{v}}=\sum_{ijk}\tau^{i}_{j}v^{j}_{|k}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{k}
and

{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}d{\vec{v}}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}\tau^{i}_{j}v^{j}_{|i}\quad\hbox{(objective value)},

(M.9)

see (
Q.32
). Then choose a Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
, to be able to use the double matrix product

{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}:d{\vec{v}}:={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}\tau^{i}_{j}v^{i}_{|j}=[{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}{}^{T}:[d{\vec{v}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}\quad\hbox{(subjective value)}.

(M.10)

M.2.1
Objective internal power for the stress: function of
d{\vec{v}}

Usual hypothesis for the internal stress in a material:
At first order,
the power density is of the type

\psi={\underline{\underline{\tau}}}\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}d{\vec{v}},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad\psi(t,p)={\underline{\underline{\tau}}}(t,p)\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}d{\vec{v}}(t,p),\;\;\forall(t,p)\in\hbox{\large$\cal C$},

(M.11)

thus the power at
t
is

{\cal P}_{t}({\vec{v}}_{t})=\int_{p\in{\Omega_{t}}}\psi(t,p)\,d{\Omega_{t}}=\int_{p\in{\Omega_{t}}}{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}_{t}(p)\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}d{\vec{v}}_{t}(p)\,d{\Omega_{t}}.

(M.12)

M.2.2
The first Piola–Kirchhoff tensor

The Piola–Kirchhoff approach consists in transforming Eulerian quantities into Lagrangian quantities
to refer to the initial configuration.
(
M.12
) gives

{\cal P}_{t}({\vec{v}}_{t})=\int_{P\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}}\psi_{t}({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P))\,|{J^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)|\,d{\Omega_{t_{0}}}=\int_{P\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}}{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}_{t}({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P))\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}d{\vec{v}}_{t}({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P))\;{J^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)\,d{\Omega_{t_{0}}}

(M.13)

(the Jacobian
{J^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)=\det({F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P))
of
{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}
at
P
is positive for a regular motion).
The Lagrangian velocity
{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}}(t,P)={\vec{v}}_{t}({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P))
satisfies

d{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)=d{\vec{v}}_{t}(p_{t}).{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
where
p_{t}={\Phi^{t_{0}}}(t,P)
.
Thus

{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}_{t}({p_{t}})\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}d{\vec{v}}_{t}({p_{t}})={\underline{\underline{\tau}}}({p_{t}})\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}(d{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P).{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)^{-1})=({F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)^{-1}.{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}({p_{t}}))\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}d{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P).

(M.14)

Quantification: Choose a basis and a Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
,
thus

{\cal P}_{t}({\vec{v}}_{t})=\int_{P\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}}(\underbrace{{J^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P){\underline{\underline{\tau}}}({p_{t}})^{T}.{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)^{-T}}_{{P\!\!\!K^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)}):d{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)\,d{\Omega_{t_{0}}}.

(M.15)

Definition M.2

The first Piola–Kirchhoff (two point) tensor at
P\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
, relative to
{t_{0}}
,
t
and a basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
,
is the linear map
{P\!\!\!K^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})
defined by

{P\!\!\!K^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)={J^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)\,{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}_{t}({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)).{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)^{-T},\quad\hbox{where}\quad{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}={\underline{\underline{\tau}}}^{T},

(M.16)

abusively written

\framebox{$\displaystyle{P\!\!\!K}=J\,{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}.F^{-T}$}.

(M.17)

Hence

{\cal P}_{t}({\vec{v}}_{t})=\int_{{\Omega_{t_{0}}}}{P\!\!\!K^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P):d{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)\,d{\Omega_{t_{0}}}.

(M.18)

Remark M.3

The Piola–Kirchhoff tensor is not that easy to master:
Everything is quite simple in a Eulerian framework (the configuration at
t
where the laws are expressed to begin with), but then everything is made more complicated when expressed in an initial configuration (at
{t_{0}}
)…
So, when the Piola–Kirchhoff tensor is used to introduce the Lie derivatives (Eulerian type),
it makes the Lie derivative quite a mysterious mathematical object, see footnote page
1
.

Remark M.4

Continuation of the remark:
With the pull-backs, (
M.13
) reads (
{J^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
being positive)

{\cal P}(t,{\widetilde{\Phi}})=\int_{{p_{t}}\in{\Omega_{t}}}\psi_{t}({p_{t}})\,d{\Omega_{t}}=\int_{P\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}}\bigl(({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{*}\psi_{t}\bigr)(P)\,\bigl(({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{*}d{\Omega_{t}}\bigr),

(M.19)

since
(({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{*}d{\Omega_{t}})={J^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)\,d{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
and
(({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{*}\psi_{t})(P)=\psi_{t}({p_{t}})
(scalar valued functions).
It gives the Piola–Kirchhoff tensor (pull-back to the initial configuration)
since

({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{*}(\alpha_{t}.{\vec{v}}_{t})({p_{t}})=(\alpha_{t}.{\vec{v}}_{t})({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P))=\alpha_{t}({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)).{\vec{v}}_{t}({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P))
.

M.2.3
The second Piola–Kirchhoff tensor

The first Piola–Kirchhoff tensor
{P\!\!\!K}
may confuse Eulerian and Lagrangian variables, linear maps and
endomorphisms… And
{P\!\!\!K}({p_{t_{0}}})
is not symmetric:
It can’t be since
{P\!\!\!K}({p_{t_{0}}})\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})
is not an endomorphism.
To get a symmetric tensor, the second Piola–Kirchhoff tensor is defined:

Definition M.5

The second Piola–Kirchhoff tensor is the endomorphism
{S\!\!K^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}})
defined by, in short,

{S\!\!K}=F^{-1}.{P\!\!\!K}=JF^{-1}.{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}.F^{-T}.

(M.20)

Full notation:
{S\!\!K^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)=({F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P))^{-1}.{P\!\!\!K^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)={J^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)({F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P))^{-1}.{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}_{t}(p).({F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P))^{-T}
.

So if
{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}_{t}(p)\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})
is symmetric then
{S\!\!K^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}})
is symmetric.

Thus, with the pull-back of the endomorphism
d{\vec{v}}_{t}\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}},{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})
:

(({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{*}d{\vec{v}}_{t})(P)={F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)^{-1}.d{\vec{v}}_{t}({p_{t}}).{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P),

(M.21)

and with
d{\vec{v}}_{t}({p_{t}})=d{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P).{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)^{-1}
and
{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}_{t}(p)
symmetric (so
{S\!\!K^{t_{0}}_{t}}
is symmetric),

\displaystyle{{\cal P}_{t}({\vec{v}}_{t})=}

\displaystyle{{}\int_{{\Omega_{t_{0}}}}{P\!\!\!K^{t_{0}}_{t}}:d{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}\,d{\Omega_{t_{0}}}=\int_{{\Omega_{t_{0}}}}({F^{t_{0}}_{t}}.{S\!\!K^{t_{0}}_{t}}):d{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}\,d{\Omega_{t_{0}}}=\int_{{\Omega_{t_{0}}}}([{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}].[{S\!\!K^{t_{0}}_{t}}]):[d{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}]^{T}\,d{\Omega_{t_{0}}}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}\int_{{\Omega_{t_{0}}}}{S\!\!K^{t_{0}}_{t}}:((d{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{T}.{F^{t_{0}}_{t}})\,d{\Omega_{t_{0}}}=\int_{{\Omega_{t_{0}}}}{S\!\!K^{t_{0}}_{t}}:({{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}^{T}.d{{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}_{t}}+d({{\vec{V}}^{t_{0}}_{t}})^{T}.{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}\over 2})\;d{\Omega_{t_{0}}}.}

(M.22)

Remark M.6

It is a “chosen time derivative” of
{S\!\!K}(t)=J(t)F(t)^{-1}.{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}(t).F(t)^{-T}

that leads to some kind of Lie derivative as explain in books in continuum mechanics, as in footnote page
1
.

M.3
Classical hyper-elasticity:
\partial W/\partial F

E
and
F
are finite dimensional spaces,
\dim E=n
,
\dim F=m
.

M.3.1
Definition

Reminder: Consider a function

{\widehat{W}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\cal L}(E;F)&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr L&\rightarrow{\widehat{W}}(L)\crcr}}\,\right.

(M.23)

Its differential

d{\widehat{W}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\cal L}(E;F)&\rightarrow{\cal L}({\cal L}(E;F);\mathbb{R})\cr L&\rightarrow d{\widehat{W}}(L)\crcr}}\,\right\}

is defined at
L
by, in a direction
M
, cf. (
S.3
),

d{\widehat{W}}(L)(M)=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{{\widehat{W}}(L+hM)-{\widehat{W}}(L)\over h}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\partial{\widehat{W}}\over\partial L}(L)(M).

(M.24)

Also written
d{\widehat{W}}(L)(M)=d{\widehat{W}}(L).M
since
d{\widehat{W}}(L)
is linear.

Example M.7

{\widehat{W}}:F\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})\rightarrow{\widehat{W}}(F)\in\mathbb{R}
(real valued function),
with
F:={F^{t_{0}}_{t}}({p_{t_{0}}})

the deformation gradient at
\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
at
t
at
{p_{t_{0}}}
. Thus
d{\widehat{W}}(F).M=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{{\widehat{W}}(F+hM)-{\widehat{W}}(F)\over h}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\partial{\widehat{W}}\over\partial F}(F).M\in\mathbb{R}
is the derivative of
{\widehat{W}}
at
F
in a direction
M\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})
.

Example M.8

m=n
, endomorphisms
L\in{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})
, and
{\widehat{W}}(L):={\rm Tr}(L)
(the trace).
Here
d{\rm Tr}(L)(M)=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{{\rm Tr}(L+hM)-{\rm Tr}(L)\over h}={\rm Tr}(M)
(the trace is linear), thus

d{\rm Tr}(L)={\rm Tr}
for all
L
.

M.3.2
Expression with bases (quantification): The
\partial W/\partial L_{ij}

Let
({\vec{a}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
be bases in
E
and
F
, with
({\pi_{ai}})
the (covariant) dual basis of
({\vec{a}}_{i})
.
Let
({\cal L}_{ij})_{i=1,...,m\atop j=1,...,n}=({\vec{b}}_{i}\otimes{\pi_{aj}})
be the associated basis in
{\cal L}(E;F)
, i.e. the
{\cal L}_{ij}
are defined by
{\cal L}_{ij}.{\vec{a}}_{\ell}=\delta_{j\ell}{\vec{b}}_{i}
for all
i=1,...,m
and
j,\ell=1,...,n
, cf. prop.
A.41
(all the elements of the matrix
[{\cal L}_{ij}]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}
vanish except the element at the intersection of row
i
and column
j
which equals
1
). Let
L\in{\cal L}(E;F)
. The derivation of
{\widehat{W}}
at
L
in a direction
{\cal L}_{ij}
is

d{\widehat{W}}(L).{\cal L}_{ij}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{{\widehat{W}}(L+h{\cal L}_{ij})-{\widehat{W}}(L)\over h}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\partial{\widehat{W}}\over\partial L_{ij}}(L)

(M.25)

(usual notation).

Associated matrix relative to the chosen bases:

[d{\widehat{W}}(L)]_{|{\cal L}_{ij}}:=[{\partial{\widehat{W}}\over\partial L_{ij}}]_{i=1,...,m\atop j=1,...,n}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}[d{\widehat{W}}(L)]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}\quad({\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}[d{\widehat{W}}(L)_{ij}]).

(M.26)

So if
M={\sum_{i=1}^{m}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}M_{ij}{\cal L}_{ij}
then

d{\widehat{W}}(L)(M)=\sum_{ij}M_{ij}\,d{\widehat{W}}(L)({\cal L}_{ij})
since
d{\widehat{W}}(L)
is linear, so

d{\widehat{W}}(L)(M)={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}{\partial{\widehat{W}}\over\partial L_{ij}}(L)M_{ij}=[d{\widehat{W}}(L)]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}}:[M]_{|{\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}},

(M.27)

double matrix contraction. Duality notations:
d{\widehat{W}}(L)(M)=\sum_{ij}{\partial{\widehat{W}}\over\partial L^{i}_{j}}(L)M^{i}_{j}
.

Remark M.9

The notation
[M]_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{e}}}:[d{\widehat{W}}(L)]_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{e}}}
is just a matrix product, since
M={\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{m}}})
and
d{\widehat{W}}(L)\in{\cal L}({\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{m}}});\mathbb{R})
are different kinds of mathematical objects.

Example M.10

Continuing example
M.8
with
({\vec{e}}_{i})=({\vec{E}}_{i})
: Then

{\widehat{W}}(L)={\rm Tr}(L)
gives

d{\widehat{W}}(L).M={\rm Tr}(M)=\sum_{i}M_{ii}
, thus
{\partial{\widehat{W}}\over\partial L_{ij}}(L)=\delta_{ij}
for all
i,j
, thus

[d{\widehat{W}}(L)]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[I]=[{\partial{\rm Tr}\over\partial L_{ij}}(L)]
(identity matrix), and we recover

d{\rm Tr}(L)(M)=[{\partial{\rm Tr}\over\partial L_{ij}}(L)]:[M]=[I]:[M]={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}M_{ii}={\rm Tr}(M)
.

Example M.11

Continuing example
M.7
:
The meaning of the derivation
{\partial{\widehat{W}}\over\partial F_{ij}}
is intriguing:
It is a derivation in the direction
{\cal L}_{ij}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes\pi_{Ej}
, where
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is a basis at
p={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
and
(\pi_{Ej})
is the dual basis of a basis
({\vec{E}}_{j})
at
P
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
, i.e.
{\partial{\widehat{W}}\over\partial F_{ij}}(F)=d{\widehat{W}}(F).{\cal L}_{ij}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}d{\widehat{W}}(F).({\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes\pi_{Ej})
is a derivation
“at the same time” in the directions
{\vec{e}}_{i}
(at
(t,p)
) and
\pi_{Ej}
(at
({t_{0}},P)
),
where
F
stands for
{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
.

M.3.3
Motions and
\omega
-lemma

Generalization of (
M.23
) to
C^{1}
functions, with
{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}
open subset in a affine space which associated vector space is
E
,

{\widehat{W}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{{\cal U}_{\!{\cal E}}}\times{\cal L}(E;F)&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr(P,L)&\rightarrow{\widehat{W}}(P,L).\cr}}\,\right.

(M.28)

At
P
, let
{\widehat{W}}_{P}(L):={\widehat{W}}(P,L)
.
The differential
d{\widehat{W}}_{P}(L){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\partial_{2}{\widehat{W}}(P,L)

in a direction
M\in{\cal L}(E;F)
is

\partial_{2}{\widehat{W}}(P,L).M:=d({\widehat{W}}_{P})(L).M=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{{\widehat{W}}(P,L+hM)-{\widehat{W}}(P,L)\over h}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\partial{\widehat{W}}\over\partial L}(P,L).M.

(M.29)

With a motion
\Phi:={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}:{\Omega_{t_{0}}}\rightarrow{\Omega_{t}}

define

f:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr C^{1}(\overline{{\Omega_{t_{0}}}};\overline{{\Omega_{t}}})&\rightarrow C^{0}(\overline{{\Omega_{t_{0}}}};\mathbb{R})\cr\Phi&\rightarrow f(\Phi):={\widehat{W}}(.,d\Phi(.)),\crcr}}\,\right.

(M.30)

a function of
\Phi
which only depends on its first (covariant) gradient; So, for all
P\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
,

f(\Phi)(P)={\widehat{W}}(P,d\Phi(P))\in\mathbb{R}.

(M.31)

(This kind of relation is generally deduced after application of the frame invariance principle, and the hypothesis of dependence on only the first order derivative
d\Phi=F
.)

Lemma M.12
(
\omega
-lemma)

For all
\Phi,\Psi\in C^{1}(\overline{{\Omega_{t_{0}}}};\overline{{\Omega_{t}}})
,

\framebox{$\displaystyle df(\Phi)(\Psi)=\partial_{2}{\widehat{W}}(.,d\Phi)(d\Psi){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\partial{\widehat{W}}\over\partial F}(.,d\Phi)(d\Psi)$},

(M.32)

i.e., for all
P\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
,

df(\Phi)(\Psi)(P)={\partial{\widehat{W}}\over\partial F}(P,d\Phi(P))(d\Psi(P)).

With bases
({\vec{E}}_{i})
and
({\vec{e}}_{i})
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
and
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
and
d\Psi.{\vec{E}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\partial\Psi_{i}\over\partial X_{j}}{\vec{e}}_{i}
, we get

df(\Phi)(\Psi)={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}{\partial{\widehat{W}}\over\partial F_{ij}}(.,d\Phi){\partial\Psi_{i}\over\partial X_{j}}(.){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}[{\partial{\widehat{W}}\over\partial F_{ij}}]:[{\partial\Psi_{i}\over\partial X_{j}}].

(M.33)

Marsden notations:

df(\Phi)(\Psi)={\sum_{i,J=1}^{n}}{\partial{\widehat{W}}\over\partial F^{i}_{J}}{\partial\Psi^{i}\over\partial X^{J}}=[{\partial{\widehat{W}}\over\partial F^{i}_{J}}]:[{\partial\Psi^{i}\over\partial X^{J}}]
.

Proof.
C^{1}(\overline{{\Omega_{t_{0}}}};\overline{{\Omega_{t}}})
is a vector space, so
df(\Phi)(\Psi)=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{f(\Phi{+}h\Psi)-f(\Phi)\over h}\in C^{0}(\overline{{\Omega_{t_{0}}}};\overline{{\Omega_{t}}})
, i.e., for any
P\in\overline{{\Omega_{t_{0}}}}
we have

df(\Phi)(\Psi)(P)=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{f(\Phi{+}h\Psi)(P)-f(\Phi)(P)\over h}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{{\widehat{W}}(P,d\Phi(P){+}h\,d\Psi(P))-{\widehat{W}}(P,d\Phi(P))\over h}=d{\vec{W}}_{P}(d\Psi(P)
, i.e. (
M.32
)

M.3.4
Application to classical hyper-elasticity:
{P\!\!\!K}=\partial W/\partial F

Let
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
be a unique Euclidean dot product in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
at all times
t
,
and let
({\vec{E}}_{i})
and
({\vec{e}}_{i})
be Euclidean bases at
{t_{0}}
and at
t
.
Let
{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}_{t}(p)=
the Cauchy stress tensor at
t
at
p={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
.

Let
{P\!\!\!K}(P)=J(P)\,{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}_{t}(p).F^{-T}(P)=

the first Piola–Kirchhoff (two point) tensor at
P
, cf. (
M.16
).
Since
{P\!\!\!K}
depends on
\Phi
, the full notation is

{P\!\!\!K}={P\!\!\!K}(\Phi)
given by

{P\!\!\!K}(\Phi)(P)=J(P)\,{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}_{t}(\Phi(P)).d\Phi(P)^{-T}.

(M.34)

Definition M.13

If there exists a function
{\widehat{{P\!\!\!K}}}
such that
{P\!\!\!K}
reads

{P\!\!\!K}(\Phi)(P)={\widehat{{P\!\!\!K}}}(P,d\Phi(P))

(M.35)

then
{\widehat{{P\!\!\!K}}}
is called a constitutive function.
(First order hypothesis:
{\widehat{{P\!\!\!K}}}
only depends on
d\Phi=F
the first order derivative of
\Phi
.)

Definition M.14

The material is hyper-elastic iff there exists a function

{\widehat{W}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\Omega_{t_{0}}}\times{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}})&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr(P,L)&\rightarrow{\widehat{W}}(P,L)\crcr}}\,\right\}

such that

({P\!\!\!K}(\Phi)=)\qquad{\widehat{{P\!\!\!K}}}(.,d\Phi)={\partial{\widehat{W}}\over\partial F}(.,d\Phi),\quad\hbox{written}\quad{\widehat{{P\!\!\!K}}}={\partial{\widehat{W}}\over\partial F},

(M.36)

that is,
{\widehat{{P\!\!\!K}}}(P,F(P))={\partial{\widehat{W}}\over\partial F}(P,F(P))
for all
P\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
, where
F=d\Phi
.

With bases
({\vec{E}}_{I})
and
({\vec{e}}_{i})
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
and
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
, and
(E^{I})
the dual basis of
({\vec{E}}_{I})
,
and
{P\!\!\!K}={\sum_{i,J=1}^{n}}{P\!\!\!K}^{i}_{J}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes E^{J}
,

[{P\!\!\!K}(\Phi)]_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{e}}}=[{\widehat{{P\!\!\!K}}}(.,F)]_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{e}}}=[{\partial{\widehat{W}}\over\partial F}(.,F)]_{|{\vec{E}},{\vec{e}}},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad[{P\!\!\!K}^{i}_{J}]=[{\partial{\widehat{W}}\over\partial F^{i}_{J}}(.,F)].

(M.37)

Thus, for any (virtual) motion
\Psi:{\Omega_{t_{0}}}\rightarrow{\Omega_{t}}
, with (
M.32
) and (
M.27
),

{\widehat{{P\!\!\!K}}}(d\Phi)(d\Psi)={\partial{\widehat{W}}\over\partial F}(d\Phi)(d\Psi)\qquad=\sum_{iJ}{\partial{\widehat{W}}\over\partial F^{i}_{J}}(F){\partial\Psi^{i}\over\partial X^{J}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}[{\widehat{{P\!\!\!K}}}]:[d\Psi],

(M.38)

that is,

{\widehat{{P\!\!\!K}}}(d\Phi)(d\Psi)(P)=\sum_{iJ}{\partial{\widehat{W}}\over\partial F^{i}_{J}}(P,{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)){\partial\Psi^{i}\over\partial X^{J}}(P)
for all
P\in{\Omega_{t_{0}}}
.

Exercice M.15

With a unique Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
both in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
and
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
,
with Euclidean bases
({\vec{E}}_{i})\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}}
and
({\vec{e}}_{i})\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t}}
, and with
(E^{i})
the dual basis of
({\vec{E}}_{i})
,
with
C=F^{T}.F
, prove (derivation in the direction
{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes E^{J}
):

\displaystyle{{\partial C\over\partial F^{i}_{J}}(F)=\sum_{K}F^{i}_{K}{\vec{E}}_{J}\otimes E^{K}+\sum_{K}F^{i}_{K}{\vec{E}}_{K}\otimes E^{J}\quad(=dC(F).({\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes E^{J})).}

(M.39)

{\partial\sqrt{C}\over\partial F}(F)={1\over 2}\bigl(\sqrt{C(F)}\,\bigr)^{-1}.{\partial C\over\partial F}(F).

(M.40)

{\partial\sqrt{C}\over\partial C}={1\over 2}(\sqrt{C})^{-1}.

(M.41)

Answer
. Let
F=\sum_{iJ}F^{i}_{J}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes E^{J}
,
so
F^{T}=\sum_{Ij}(F^{T})^{I}_{j}{\vec{E}}_{I}\otimes e^{j}=\sum_{Ij}F^{j}_{I}{\vec{E}}_{I}\otimes e^{j}
,
and

C=\sum_{IJ}C^{I}_{J}{\vec{E}}_{i}\otimes E^{J}=F^{T}.F=\sum_{IJ}\sum_{k}(F^{T})^{I}_{k}F^{k}_{J}{\vec{E}}_{I}\otimes E^{J}=\sum_{IJ}\sum_{k}F^{k}_{I}F^{k}_{J}{\vec{E}}_{I}\otimes E^{J}=C(F)
,
so
C^{I}_{J}=\sum_{k}F^{k}_{I}F^{k}_{J}=C^{I}_{J}(F)
.
And

\displaystyle{C(F+h{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes E^{J})=}

\displaystyle{{}(F+h{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes E^{J})^{T}.(F+h{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes E^{J})=(F^{T}+h{\vec{E}}_{J}\otimes e^{i}).(F+h{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes E^{J})}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}C(F)+h\,({\vec{E}}_{J}\otimes e^{i}).F+h\,F^{T}.({\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes E^{J})+h^{2}\,{\vec{E}}_{J}\otimes E^{i}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}C(F)+h\,(\sum_{K}F^{i}_{K}{\vec{E}}_{J}\otimes E^{K}+\sum_{K}(F^{T})^{K}_{i}{\vec{E}}_{K}\otimes E^{J})+h^{2}\,{\vec{E}}_{J}\otimes E^{J}}

(M.42)

Thus (
M.39
). And

C(F+h{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes E^{J})-C(F)=(\sqrt{C}(F+h{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes E^{J})+\sqrt{C}(F)).(\sqrt{C}(F+h{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes E^{J})-\sqrt{C}(F))
gives

dC(F)({\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes E^{J})=2\sqrt{C}(F).d\sqrt{C}(F)({\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes E^{J})
.
Thus
{\partial\sqrt{C}\over\partial F^{i}_{J}}(F)={1\over 2}(\sqrt{C(F)})^{-1}.{\partial C\over\partial F^{i}_{J}}(F)
.

(C+h{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j})-C=(\sqrt{C+h{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}}+\sqrt{C}).(\sqrt{C+h{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}}-\sqrt{C})
, divided by
h
,
gives

{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}=2\sqrt{C}.\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{\sqrt{C+h{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}}-\sqrt{C}\over h}=2\sqrt{C}.d\sqrt{C}.({\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j})
,
thus
L=2\sqrt{C}.(d\sqrt{C}.L)
for all
L
(linearity of
d\sqrt{C}
),
thus
d\sqrt{C}.L={1\over 2}(\sqrt{C})^{-1}.L
.

M.3.5
Corollary (hyper-elasticity):
{S\!\!K}=\partial W/\partial C

With the symmetry of the second Piola–Kirchhoff tensor
{S\!\!K}=F^{-1}.{P\!\!\!K}
,
we deduce
{S\!\!K^{t_{0}}_{t}}({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}})(P)={\widehat{{S\!\!K^{t_{0}}_{t}}}}(P,{F^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P))
(constitutive function).
And we deduce the existence of a function

{\widetilde{W}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\Omega_{t_{0}}}\times{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{t_{0}}})&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr(P,L)&\rightarrow{\widetilde{W}}(P,L)\crcr}}\,\right\}

such that,

{\widehat{{S\!\!K^{t_{0}}_{t}}}}(.,C)={\partial{\widetilde{W}}\over\partial C}(.,C).

(M.43)

(See Marsden and Hughes
[
14
]
for details and the thermodynamical hypotheses required.)

Appendix N
Conservation of mass

Let
\rho(t,p)=\rho_{t}(p)
be the (Eulerian) mass density at
t
at
p\in{\Omega_{t}}
, supposed to be
>0
;
The mass
m({\omega_{t}})
of a subset
{\omega_{t}}\subset{\Omega_{t}}
is

m({\omega_{t}})=\int_{p\in{\omega_{t}}}\rho_{t}(p)\,d{\omega_{t}}.

(N.1)

Conservation of mass principle
(no loss nor production of particles): For all
\omega_{t_{0}}\subset\Omega_{t_{0}}
and all
t
,

m(\omega_{t})=m(\omega_{t_{0}}),\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad\int_{p\in\omega_{t}}\rho_{t}(p)\,d\omega_{t}=\int_{P\in{\omega_{t_{0}}}}\rho_{t_{0}}(P)\,d\omega_{t_{0}}.

(N.2)

Proposition N.1

If (
N.2
) then, with
{J^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)=\det(d{\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P))
(positive Jacobian the motion being supposed regular) and
p={\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)
,

\rho_{t}(p)={\rho_{t_{0}}(P)\over{J^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)}.

(N.3)

Proof.
The change of variable formula gives

\int_{p\in{\omega_{t}}}\rho_{t}(p)\,d\omega_{t}=\int_{P\in\omega_{t_{0}}}\rho_{t}({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P))\,{J^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)\,d\omega_{t_{0}},

thus (
N.2
) gives
\rho_{t}({\Phi^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)){J^{t_{0}}_{t}}(P)=\rho_{t_{0}}(P)
.

Proposition N.2

{\vec{v}}={\vec{v}}(t,{p_{t}})
being the Eulerian velocity at
(t,{p_{t}})\in\mathbb{R}\times{\Omega_{t}}
, (
N.2
) gives

{D\rho\over Dt}+\rho\,{\rm div}{\vec{v}}=0,\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad{\partial\rho\over\partial t}+{\rm div}(\rho\,{\vec{v}})=0.

(N.4)

Thus, for all
{\omega_{t}}\subset{\Omega_{t}}
,

\int_{\omega_{t}}{\partial\rho\over\partial t}\,d\omega_{t}=-\int_{\partial\omega_{t}}\rho{\vec{v}}.{\vec{n}}\,d\sigma_{t}.

(N.5)

Proof.
(
N.2
) gives

{d\over dt}(\int_{p(t)\in\omega_{t}}\rho(t,p(t))\,d\omega_{t})=0,

and Leibniz formula (
K.41
) applied for all
{\omega_{t}}
gives (
N.4
).
Then the Green formula

\int_{\Omega_{t}}{\rm div}(\rho\,{\vec{v}})\,d\Omega_{t}=\int_{\partial\Omega_{t}}\rho{\vec{v}}.{\vec{n}}\,d\sigma_{t}

gives (
N.5
).

Exercice N.3

Use (
N.3
) to prove (
N.4
).

Answer
.
J(t,P)\rho(t,\Phi(t,P))=\rho_{t_{0}}(P)
give, with
{p_{t}}=\Phi(t,P)
,

{\partial J\over\partial t}(t,P)\,\rho(t,{p_{t}})+J(t,P)\,\Bigl({\partial\rho\over\partial t}(t,{p_{t}})+d\rho(t,{p_{t}}).d\Phi(t,P)\Bigr)=0.

Thus

{\partial J\over\partial t}(t,P)=J(t,P)\,{\rm div}{\vec{v}}(t,p)
, cf. (
K.40
),
gives (
N.4
).

Appendix O
Balance of momentum

O.1
Framework

{\widetilde{\Phi}}:[{t_{0}},T]\times{\it O\!b\!j}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
is a regular motion,

{\Omega_{t}}={\widetilde{\Phi}}(t,{\it O\!b\!j})
,
{\Gamma_{t}}=\partial{\Omega_{t}}
(the boundary),

{\vec{v}}
is the Eulerian velocity field,

{\omega_{t}}
is a regular sub domain in
{\Omega_{t}}
and
{\partial\omega_{t}}
is its boundary.

An observer chooses a Euclidean basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
(e.g. made with the foot or the metre)
and call
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
the associated Euclidean dot product.
And
{\vec{n}}(t,p)={\vec{n}}_{t}(p)
is the outer unit normal at
t
at
p\in{\partial\omega_{t}}
.

All the functions are assumed to be regular enough to validate the following calculations.

Let
\rho:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\bigcup_{t\in[{t_{0}},T]}(\{t\}\times{\Omega_{t}})&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr(t,{p_{t}})&\rightarrow\rho(t,{p_{t}})\cr}}\,\right\}

(a mass density),
let
{\vec{f}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\bigcup_{t\in[{t_{0}},T]}(\{t\}\times{\Omega_{t}})&\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\cr(t,{p_{t}})&\rightarrow{\vec{f}}(t,{p_{t}})\cr}}\,\right\}

(a body force density),
and
let
\vec{T}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\bigcup_{t\in[{t_{0}},T]}(\{t\}\times{\partial\omega_{t}}\times{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{t}}})&\rightarrow{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\cr(t,{p_{t}},{\vec{n}}({p_{t}}))&\rightarrow\vec{T}(t,{p_{t}},{\vec{n}}({p_{t}}))\cr}}\,\right\}

(a surface force density) defined for any regular subset
{\omega_{t}}\subset{\Omega_{t}}
.

O.2
Master balance law

Definition O.1

The balance of momentum is satisfied by
\rho
,
{\vec{f}}
and
\vec{T}
iff, for all regular open subset
{\omega_{t}}
in
{\Omega_{t}}
,

{d\over dt}(\int_{\omega_{t}}\rho{\vec{v}}\,d{\Omega_{t}})=\int_{\omega_{t}}{\vec{f}}\,d{\Omega_{t}}+\int_{\partial\omega_{t}}\vec{T}_{\partial\omega_{t}}\,d{\Gamma_{t}}\quad\hbox{(master balance law)}.

(O.1)

(It is in fact a linearity hypothesis, see theorem
O.2
.)

Thus, with (
K.41
),

\int_{\omega_{t}}{D(\rho{\vec{v}})\over Dt}+\rho{\vec{v}}\,{\rm div}{\vec{v}}\;d{\Omega_{t}}=\int_{\omega_{t}}{\vec{f}}\,d{\Omega_{t}}+\int_{\partial\omega_{t}}\vec{T}_{\partial\omega_{t}}\,d{\Gamma_{t}}.

(O.2)

And with the conservation of mass hypothesis, cf. (
N.4
), we get

\int_{\omega_{t}}\rho{D{\vec{v}}\over Dt}\;d{\Omega_{t}}=\int_{\omega_{t}}{\vec{f}}\,d{\Omega_{t}}+\int_{\partial\omega_{t}}\vec{T}_{\partial\omega_{t}}\,d{\Gamma_{t}},

(O.3)

with
{D{\vec{v}}\over Dt}={\vec{\gamma}}=
the Eulerian acceleration.

O.3
Cauchy theorem
\vec{T}={\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}.{\vec{n}}
(stress tensor
{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}
)

Theorem O.2
(Cauchy first law: Cauchy stress tensor)

If the master balance law (
O.1
) is satisfied, then

\vec{T}
is linear in
{\vec{n}}
, that is, there exists a Eulerian endomorphism

{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}
, identified to a Eulerian tensor
{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}\in{T^{1}_{1}({\Omega_{t}})}
,
called the Cauchy stress tensor, s.t. on all
{\partial\omega_{t}}
, in short

\vec{T}={\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}.{\vec{n}},

(O.4)

where
{\vec{n}}
is the unit outward normal to
{\partial\omega_{t}}
(i.e.,

\vec{T}(t,{p_{t}})={\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}(t,{p_{t}}).{\vec{n}}(t,{p_{t}})
for all
t
and
{p_{t}}\in{\partial\omega_{t}}
).

The proof is based on:

Lemma O.3

Let

\varphi:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\overline{\Omega}}&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr p&\rightarrow\varphi(p)\crcr}}\,\right\}\in C^{1}({\overline{\Omega}};\mathbb{R})

and

\psi:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\overline{\Omega}}\times{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr(p,{\vec{w}})&\rightarrow\psi(p,{\vec{w}})\crcr}}\,\right\}\in C^{1}({\overline{\Omega}},{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}};\mathbb{R})
. If

\forall\omega\hbox{ open in }\Omega,\;\int_{p\in\omega}\varphi(p)\,d\Omega=\int_{p\in{\partial\omega}}\psi(p,{\vec{n}}(p))\,d\Gamma

(O.5)

(no dependence on the curvature or on higher derivatives since at any
p\in{\partial\omega}
,
\psi
only depends on
{\vec{n}}(p)
),
then

\exists{\vec{k}}\in C^{1}({\overline{\Omega}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}^{3}}})\hbox{ s{.}t{.}\ }\psi=({\vec{k}},{\vec{n}})_{g},\hbox{ and }\varphi={\rm div}{\vec{k}},

(O.6)

i.e.
\psi
depends linearly on
{\vec{n}}
, and
\varphi
is a divergence.

Proof.
(Lemma
O.3
.)
(This proof is standard: We recall it.)
Let
p\in\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
.
Consider the tetrahedral defined by its vertices

p
,
p+(h_{1},0,0)
,
p+(0,h_{2},0)
and
p+(0,0,h_{3})
, with
h_{i}>0
for all
i
.
(On each face of a tetrahedron, the unit normal vector is uniform.)
Let
\Sigma_{1}
the side which outer unit normal is
-{\vec{E}}_{1}
:
It area is
\sigma_{1}={1\over 2}h_{2}h_{3}
(square triangle).
Idem for
\Sigma_{2}
and
\Sigma_{3}
.
Let
\Sigma
be the fourth side: its area is

\sigma={1\over 2}\sqrt{h_{2}^{2}h_{3}^{2}+h_{3}^{2}h_{1}^{2}+h_{1}^{2}h_{2}^{2}}
and
its outer unit normal is
{\vec{n}}={1\over 2\sigma}(h_{2}h_{3},h_{3}h_{1},h_{1}h_{2})
(see exercise
O.5
),
that is
{\vec{n}}=(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})
with
n_{i}={\sigma_{i}\over\sigma}
pour
i=1,2,3
.
The volume of the tetrahedral is
{1\over 6}h_{1}h_{2}h_{3}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\ell^{3}
. Let
M:=\sup_{p\in{\overline{\Omega}}}|\varphi(p)|
; We have
M<\infty
, since
\varphi
is continuous in
{\overline{\Omega}}
. Then (
O.6
) give

M\ell^{3}\geq|\int_{\partial\omega_{t}}\psi(p,{\vec{n}}(p))\,d\Gamma|,\quad\hbox{so}\quad\int_{\partial\omega_{t}}\psi(p,{\vec{n}}(p))\,d\Gamma=O(\ell^{3}).

(O.7)

And
\psi
being continuous, the mean value theorem applied on
\Sigma_{i}
gives: There exists
p_{i}\in\Sigma_{i}
s.t.

\int_{\Sigma_{i}}\psi(p,{\vec{n}}(p))\,d\Gamma=\sigma_{i}\psi(p_{i},{\vec{n}}_{i}).

Thus

\int_{\partial\omega_{t}}\psi(p,{\vec{n}}(p))\,d\Gamma=\Bigl(\sigma_{1}\psi(p_{1},-{\vec{E}}_{1})+\sigma_{2}\psi(t,p_{2},-{\vec{E}}_{2})+\sigma_{3}\psi(p_{3},-{\vec{E}}_{3})+\sigma\psi(p_{4},{\vec{n}})\Bigr).

Then,
\Psi
being continous, (
O.7
) gives

\sigma_{1}\psi(p_{1},-{\vec{E}}_{1})+\sigma_{2}\psi(p_{2},-{\vec{E}}_{2})+\sigma_{3}\psi(p_{3},-{\vec{E}}_{3})+\sigma\psi(p_{4},{\vec{n}})=O(\ell^{3}).

(O.8)

We flatten the tetrahedron on the
yz
face by taking
h_{2}=h_{3}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}h
and
h_{1}=h^{2}
;
Thus
\sigma_{1}={1\over 2}h^{2}
,
\sigma_{2}=o(h^{2})
,
\sigma_{3}=o(h^{2})
,

\sigma\sim\sigma_{1}
,
\ell^{3}={1\over 6}h^{4}
, with
{\vec{n}}\sim-{\vec{n}}_{1}={\vec{E}}_{1}

and
p_{i}\sim p
; Then

\psi(p,-{\vec{E}}_{1})+\psi(p,+{\vec{E}}_{1})=0.

(O.9)

Idem with
xz
and
xy
.
And for a fixed tetrahedron with
h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}
given,
consider the smaller tetrahedron with
\varepsilon h_{1},\varepsilon h_{2},\varepsilon h_{3}
.
Then as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
(
O.8
) with (
O.9
) give

\displaystyle{\psi(p,{\vec{n}})=}

\displaystyle{{}-{\sigma_{i}\over\sigma}\psi(p,-{\vec{E}}_{1})-{\sigma_{2}\over\sigma}\psi(p,-{\vec{E}}_{2})-{\sigma_{3}\over\sigma}\psi(p,-{\vec{E}}_{3})=\sum_{i=1}^{3}n_{i}\psi(p,{\vec{E}}_{i}),}

since
n_{i}={\sigma_{i}\over\sigma}
pour
i=1,2,3
.
The same steps can be done for any (inclined) tetrahedron (or apply a change of variable to get back to the above tetrahedron).
Thus
\psi_{p}
is a linear map in
{\vec{n}}_{p}
,
that is, there exists a linear form
\alpha_{p}
s.t.
\psi_{p}({\vec{n}}_{p})=\alpha_{p}.{\vec{n}}_{p}
for any
p\in{\partial\omega}
.
And the Riesz representation theorem gives:

\exists{\vec{k}}_{p}
s.t.
\alpha_{p}.{\vec{n}}_{p}=({\vec{k}}_{p},{\vec{n}}_{p})_{g}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{k}}_{p}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{\vec{n}}_{p}
.

Proof.
(Theorem.)
Apply Lemma
O.3
component by component with

\vec{\varphi}=\rho{D{\vec{v}}\over Dt}-{\vec{f}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\varphi^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,
cf. (
O.3
).

Corollary O.4

With
{\rm div}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}:={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}({\sum_{j=1}^{n}}{\partial\sigma_{ij}\over\partial x_{j}}){\vec{e}}_{i}

(definition of “the matrix divergence” see (
S.65
)),

\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&{\vec{f}}+{\rm div}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}=\rho{D{\vec{v}}\over Dt}\quad\hbox{in }{\Omega_{t}},\cr&{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}.{\vec{n}}=\vec{T}\quad\hbox{on }{\Gamma_{t}}\crcr}}\,\right.

(O.10)

(matrix meaning).
(With duality notations,

{\rm div}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}:={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}({\sum_{j=1}^{n}}{\partial\sigma^{i}_{j}\over\partial x^{j}}){\vec{e}}_{i}
.)

Proof.
Apply the divergence Formula to (
O.3
).

Exercice O.5

Consider a triangle
T
in
{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
which vertices are
A=(h_{1},0,0)
,
B=(0,h_{2},0)
,
C=(0,0,h_{3})
.
Prove that
{\vec{n}}=(h_{2}h_{3},h_{3}h_{1},h_{1}h_{2})
is orthogonal to
T

and that
\sigma={1\over 2}\sqrt{h_{2}^{2}h_{3}^{2}+h_{3}^{2}h_{1}^{2}+h_{1}^{2}h_{2}^{2}}
is its area.

Answer
. Consider the parametric surface
{\vec{r}}(t,u)=A+t\vec{AB}+u\vec{AC}
for
t,u\in[0,1]

describing the triangle. Thus

{\vec{n}}={\partial{\vec{r}}\over\partial t}\wedge{\partial{\vec{r}}\over\partial u}=\vec{AB}\wedge\vec{AC}=\pmatrix{-h_{1}\cr h_{2}\cr 0}\wedge\pmatrix{-h_{1}\cr 0\cr h_{3}}=\pmatrix{h_{2}h_{3}\cr h_{3}h_{1}\cr h_{1}h_{2}}
is orthonormal.
And
d\sigma=||{\partial{\vec{r}}\over\partial t}\wedge{\partial{\vec{r}}\over\partial u}||dudt=\sqrt{h_{2}^{2}h_{3}^{2}+h_{3}^{2}h_{1}^{2}+h_{1}^{2}h_{2}^{2}}dudt
.
Thus
\sigma=\int_{t=0}^{1}\int_{u=0}^{1}d\sigma=\sqrt{h_{2}^{2}h_{3}^{2}+h_{3}^{2}h_{1}^{2}+h_{1}^{2}h_{2}^{2}}

is twice the aera of the triangle.

Appendix P
Balance of moment of momentum

Definition P.1

The balance of moment of momentum is satisfied by
\rho
,
{\vec{f}}
and
\vec{T}
iff for all regular sub-open set
{\omega_{t}}\subset{\Omega_{t}}

{d\over dt}\int_{\omega_{t}}\rho\,\overrightarrow{{\cal O}M}\wedge{\vec{v}}\,d{\Omega_{t}}=\int_{\omega_{t}}\rho\,\overrightarrow{{\cal O}M}\wedge{\vec{f}}\,d{\Omega_{t}}+\int_{\partial\omega_{t}}\overrightarrow{{\cal O}M}\wedge\vec{T}\,d{\Gamma_{t}},

(P.1)

equality called the master balance of moment of momentum law.
(This excludes e.g. Cosserat continua materials.)

Theorem P.2

(Cauchy second law.)

If the master balance law (so
\vec{T}={\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}.{\vec{n}}
)
and the master balance of moment of momentum law are satisfied then
{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}
is symmetric.

Proof.
(Standard proof.)
Let
{\vec{x}}=\overrightarrow{{\cal O}M}=\sum_{i}x_{i}{\vec{E}}_{i}
,
and
\vec{T}=\sum_{i}T_{i}{\vec{E}}_{i}={\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}.{\vec{n}}=\sum_{ij}\sigma_{ij}n_{j}{\vec{E}}_{i}
.
Then (first component)

({\vec{x}}\wedge\vec{T})_{1}=x_{2}T_{3}-x_{3}T_{2}=x_{2}(\sigma_{31}n_{1}+\sigma_{32}n_{2}+\sigma_{33}n_{3})-x_{3}(\sigma_{21}n_{1}+\sigma_{22}n_{2}+\sigma_{23}n_{3})=(x_{2}\sigma_{31}-x_{3}\sigma_{21})n_{1}+(x_{2}\sigma_{32}-x_{3}\sigma_{22})n_{2}+(x_{2}\sigma_{33}-x_{3}\sigma_{23})n_{3}
.
Thus

\int_{\partial\omega_{t}}({\vec{x}}\wedge\vec{T})_{1}\,d{\Gamma_{t}}=\int_{\omega_{t}}{\partial(x_{2}\sigma_{31}-x_{3}\sigma_{21})\over\partial x_{1}}+{\partial(x_{2}\sigma_{32}-x_{3}\sigma_{22})\over\partial x_{2}}+{\partial(x_{2}\sigma_{33}-x_{3}\sigma_{23})\over\partial x_{3}}\,d{\Omega_{t}}=\int_{\omega_{t}}x_{2}({\rm div}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}})_{3}+x_{3}({\rm div}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}})_{2}+\sigma_{32}-\sigma_{23}\,d{\omega_{t}}
.

(
O.10
) gives
\rho{D{\vec{v}}\over Dt}-{\vec{f}}={\rm div}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}
,
thus
{\vec{x}}\wedge(\rho{\vec{\gamma}}-{\vec{f}})={\vec{x}}\wedge{\rm div}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}
,
so the first component of
{\vec{x}}\wedge(\rho{\vec{\gamma}}-{\vec{f}})
is

x_{2}({\rm div}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}})_{3}-x_{3}({\rm div}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}})_{2}
, cf. (
O.10
).
Thus (
P.1
) gives
\int_{\omega_{t}}\sigma_{32}-\sigma_{23}\,d{\omega_{t}}=0
.
True for all
{\omega_{t}}
, thus
\sigma_{32}-\sigma_{23}=0
.
Idem for the other components:
{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}
is symmetric.

Appendix Q
Uniform tensors in
{\cal L}^{r}_{s}(E)

Uniform tensors enable to define without ambiguity the “objective contraction rules”.
Uniform tensors are scalar valued multilinear functions acting on both vectors and linear forms.

NB: In classical mechanics courses, what is called a “tensor” generally not a tensor but a matrix.
E.g. you may encounter the expression “Euclidean tensor” which means:
The matrix representation of “something” with respect to a Euclidean basis (based on the foot, metre,…) chosen by some observer. (An “Euclidean tensor” is a non-sense, e.g. can you define a “Euclidean vector”?)

Q.1
Tensorial product and multilinear forms

Let
A_{1},...,A_{n}
be
n
finite dimension vector spaces. And
A_{i}^{*}={\cal L}(A_{i};\mathbb{R})
the set of linear forms.

Q.1.1
Tensorial product of functions

Let
f_{1}:A_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, …,
f_{n}:A_{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be
n
functions.
Their tensorial product is the function

f_{1}\otimes...\otimes f_{n}:A_{1}\times...\times A_{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
defined by (separate variable function)

(f_{1}\otimes...\otimes f_{n})({\vec{x}}_{1},...,{\vec{x}}_{n})=f_{1}({\vec{x}}_{1})...f_{n}({\vec{x}}_{n}).

(Q.1)

(E.g.,
n=2
and
A_{1}=A_{2}=\mathbb{R}
and
(\cos\otimes\sin)(x,y)=\cos(x)\sin(y)
.)

Q.1.2
Tensorial product of linear forms: multilinear forms

Let
{\cal L}(A_{1},...,A_{n};\mathbb{R})
be the set of
\mathbb{R}
-multilinear forms on the Cartesian product

A_{1}\times...\times A_{n}
, that is, the set of the functions

M:A_{1}\times...\times A_{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
s.t.,
for all
i=1,...,n
, all
{\vec{x}}_{i},{\vec{y}}_{i}\in A_{i}
and all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,

M(...,{\vec{x}}_{i}+\lambda{\vec{y}}_{i},...)=M(...,{\vec{x}}_{i},...)+\lambda\;M(...,{\vec{y}}_{i},...),

(Q.2)

the other variables being unchanged.

Definition: An elementary tensor is multilinear form
M=\ell_{1}\otimes....\otimes\ell_{n}
, with
\ell_{i}\in A_{i}^{*}
for all
i
; So

\forall({\vec{x}}_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}^{*}}}\in\prod_{i=1}^{n}A_{i},\quad(\ell_{1}\otimes...\otimes\ell_{n})({\vec{x}}_{1},...,{\vec{x}}_{n})=(\ell_{1}.{\vec{x}}_{1})...(\ell_{n}.{\vec{x}}_{n})\;\in\mathbb{R}.

(Q.3)

(The dot in
\ell_{i}.{\vec{x}}_{i}
is
not
an inner dot product: It is the duality “outer product”
\ell_{i}.{\vec{x}}_{i}:=\ell_{i}({\vec{x}}_{i})
, cf. (
A.45
).)

Q.2
Uniform tensors in
{\cal L}^{0}_{s}(E)

Let
E
be a real vector space, with
\dim(E)=n\in{\mathbb{N}^{*}}
.
In this section we consider the first overlay on
E
made of
multilinear forms
M
on
E
, called the uniform tensors of type
0

s
or of type
{0\choose s}
.

E.g.,
M\in{\cal L}^{0}_{1}(E)
a linear form,
M\in{\cal L}^{0}_{2}(E)
an inner dot product,
M\in{\cal L}^{0}_{n}(E)
a determinant…

Notations for quantification purposes:
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is a basis in
E
,

(\pi_{ei})
is its (covariant) dual basis (basis in
{E^{*}}={\cal L}(E;\mathbb{R})
),

(\partial_{i})
is its bidual basis (basis in
{E^{**}}={\cal L}(E^{*};\mathbb{R})
).

Q.2.1
Definition of type
{0\choose s}
uniform tensors

{\cal L}^{0}_{0}(E):=\mathbb{R}
, and if
s\in{\mathbb{N}^{*}}
then

{\cal L}^{0}_{s}(E):={\cal L}(\underbrace{E\times...\times E}_{s\;\rm times};\mathbb{R})

(Q.4)

is called the set of uniform tensors of type
{0\choose s}
on
E
.

Q.2.2
Example: Type
{0\choose 1}
uniform tensor = linear forms

A type
{0\choose 1}
uniform tensor is an element of
{\cal L}^{0}_{1}(E)={\cal L}(E;\mathbb{R})={E^{*}}
:
It is a linear form
\ell\in{\cal L}^{0}_{1}(E)=E^{*}
.

Quantification:

With
\ell_{i}:=\ell({\vec{e}}_{i})
we have, cf. (
A.10
),

\ell={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\ell_{i}\pi_{ei},\quad\hbox{and}\quad[\ell]_{|\pi_{e}}=\pmatrix{\ell_{1}&...&\ell_{n}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}[\ell]_{|{\vec{e}}}

(Q.5)

(row matrix for a linear form).
Duality notations:
(e^{i})
is the covariant dual basis and
\ell={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\ell_{i}e^{i}
.

Thus, if
{\vec{v}}\in E
,
{\vec{v}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}v_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,
then
{\vec{v}}
is represented by

[{\vec{v}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{v_{1}\cr\vdots\cr v_{n}}
(column matrix for a vector), and
the matrix calculation rules give

\ell({\vec{v}})=[\ell]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{v}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{\ell_{1}&...&\ell_{n}}.\pmatrix{v_{1}\cr\vdots\cr v_{n}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\ell_{i}v_{i}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\ell.{\vec{v}}.

(Q.6)

Duality notations:
{\vec{v}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}v^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
and
\ell({\vec{v}})={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\ell_{i}v^{i}
, and Einstein’s convention is satisfied.

Q.2.3
Example: Type
{0\choose 2}
uniform tensor

A type
{0\choose 2}
uniform tensor is an element of
{\cal L}^{0}_{2}(E)={\cal L}(E,E;\mathbb{R})
:
It is a bilinear form
T\in{\cal L}(E,E;\mathbb{R})
.

Quantification:

Let
T_{ij}:=T({\vec{e}}_{i},{\vec{e}}_{j})
. Then, with
{\vec{v}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}v_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
and
{\vec{w}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}w_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,

T({\vec{v}},{\vec{w}})={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}T_{ij}v_{i}w_{j}=[{\vec{v}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}^{T}.[T]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{e}}},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad T={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}T_{ij}\pi_{ei}\otimes\pi_{ej}.

(Q.7)

Duality notations:
T({\vec{v}},{\vec{w}})={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}T_{ij}v^{i}w^{j}
,
and Einstein’s convention is satisfied.

An elementary uniform tensor in
{\cal L}^{0}_{2}(E)
is a tensor
T=\ell\otimes m
, where
\ell,m\in{E^{*}}
.
And so, for all
{\vec{v}},{\vec{w}}\in E
,

(\ell\otimes m)({\vec{v}},{\vec{w}})=(\ell.{\vec{v}})(m.{\vec{w}}).

(Q.8)

Q.2.4
Example: Determinant

The determinant is a alternating
{0\choose n}
uniform tensor, cf. (
K.2
).

Q.3
Uniform tensors in
{\cal L}^{r}_{s}(E)

In this section we consider an over-overlay on
E
:
The multilinear forms acting on both vectors (
\in E
) and functions
\in{E^{*}}
(linear forms).

Q.3.1
Definition of type
{r\choose s}
uniform tensors

Let
r,s\in\mathbb{N}
s.t.
r+s\geq 1
. The set of multilinear forms

{\cal L}^{r}_{s}(E):={\cal L}(\underbrace{E^{*}\times...\times E^{*}}_{r\;\rm times},\underbrace{E\times...\times E}_{s\;\rm times};\mathbb{R})

(Q.9)

is called the set of uniform tensors of type
{r\choose s}
on
E
.

The case
r=0
has been considered at §
Q.2
.

When
r\geq 1
, a tensor
T\in{\cal L}^{r}_{s}(E)
is a functional:
Its domain of definition contains a set of functions (the set
{E^{*}}={\cal L}(E;\mathbb{R})
).

Q.3.2
Example: Type
{1\choose 0}
uniform tensor: Identified with a vector

A uniform
{1\choose 0}
tensor is a element
T\in{\cal L}^{1}_{0}(E)={\cal L}({E^{*}};\mathbb{R})={\cal L}({\cal L}(E;\mathbb{R});\mathbb{R})={E^{**}}
.
With the natural canonical isomorphism

{\cal J}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr E&\rightarrow{E^{**}}={\cal L}^{1}_{0}(E)\cr{\vec{w}}&\rightarrow{\cal J}({\vec{w}})=w,\quad\hbox{defined by}\quad w(\ell):=\ell({\vec{w}}),\quad\forall\ell\in{E^{*}},\crcr}}\,\right.

(Q.10)

cf. (
T.9
) and prop.
T.5
,

w{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{w}},\quad\hbox{so}\quad w.\ell{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{w}}.\ell\quad(=\ell.{\vec{w}}).

(Q.11)

So a
{1\choose 0}
type uniform tensor
w
is identified (natural canonical) to the vector
{\vec{w}}={\cal J}^{-1}(w)
.

Interpretation:

{E^{**}}
is the set of directional derivatives.
Indeed, if
{\cal E}
is an affine space, if
E
is the associated vector space,
if
p\in{\cal E}
, and if
f
is a differentiable function at
p
,
then
w.df(p)\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqisomcan00})}df(p).{\vec{w}}
is the directional derivative along
{\vec{w}}
.

Remark: In differential geometry,
w.df
is written
{\vec{w}}(f)
, so
{\vec{w}}(f)(p):=df(p).{\vec{w}}
, the definition of a vector being a directional derivative.

Quantification:
For all
i,j
,

\partial_{i}.\pi_{ej}=\delta_{ij}=\pi_{ej}.{\vec{e}}_{i},\quad\hbox{thus}\quad\partial_{i}={\cal J}({\vec{e}}_{i}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{e}}_{i}.

(Q.12)

Duality notations:
\partial_{i}.e^{j}=\delta^{j}_{i}=e^{j}.{\vec{e}}_{i}
.
E.g., if
f
is a
C^{1}
function then
df(p)={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}f_{|i}(p)\,\pi_{ei}
(
={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}f_{|i}(p)\,e^{i}
) and

\partial_{i}(df(p))=df(p).{\vec{e}}_{i}=f_{|i}(p){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\partial_{i}(f)(p){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{e}}_{i}(f)(p).

(Q.13)

Q.3.3
Example: Type
{1\choose 1}
uniform tensor

An elementary uniform tensor in
{\cal L}^{1}_{1}(E)
is a tensor
T=u\otimes\beta
,
where
u\in{E^{**}}
and
\beta\in{E^{*}}
.
And, with
{\vec{u}}=J^{-1}(u)\in E
, cf. (
Q.10
),
we also write
T={\vec{u}}\otimes\beta
. Thus, for all
\ell\in{E^{*}}
and
{\vec{w}}\in E

(u\otimes\beta)(\ell,{\vec{w}})=u(\ell)\beta({\vec{w}})=\ell({\vec{u}})\beta({\vec{w}}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{u}}(\ell)\beta({\vec{w}}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}({\vec{u}}\otimes\beta)(\ell,{\vec{w}}).

(Q.14)

Quantification:

Let
T(\pi_{ei},{\vec{e}}_{j})
. So

T={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}T_{ij}\;{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes\pi_{ej},\quad\hbox{and}\quad[T]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[T_{ij}],

(Q.15)

[T]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[T_{ij}]
being the matrix of
T
relative to the basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
.
Duality notations:
T(e^{i},{\vec{e}}_{j})=T^{i}{}_{j}
,
[T]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[T^{i}{}_{j}]
,
T={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}T^{i}{}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
, and
Einstein’s convention is satisfied.

Thus with
\ell\in{E^{*}}
,
\ell={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\ell_{i}e^{i}\in{E^{*}}
, and
{\vec{w}}\in E
,
{\vec{w}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}w^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}\in E
, (
Q.15
) gives

T(\ell,{\vec{w}})={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}T_{ij}{\vec{e}}_{i}(\ell)\pi_{ej}({\vec{w}})={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}T_{ij}\ell_{i}w_{j}=[\ell]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[T]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}

(Q.16)

(
[\ell]_{|{\vec{e}}}
is a row matrix). Duality notations:

T(\ell,{\vec{w}})={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}T^{i}{}_{j}\ell_{i}w^{j}
and Einstein convention is satisfied.

Q.3.4
Example: Type
{1\choose 2}
uniform tensor

The same steps are applied to any tensor.
E.g., if
T\in{\cal L}^{1}_{2}(E)
, then with duality notations,
T^{i}{}_{\!jk}=T(e^{i},{\vec{e}}_{j},{\vec{e}}_{k})
and

T={\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}}T^{i}{}_{\!jk}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}\otimes e^{k},\quad\hbox{and}\quad T(\ell,{\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})={\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}}T^{i}{}_{\!jk}\ell_{i}u^{j}w^{k}.

(Q.17)

Q.4
Exterior tensorial products

Let
T_{1}\in{\cal L}^{r_{1}}_{s_{1}}(E)
and
T_{2}\in{\cal L}^{r_{2}}_{s_{2}}(E)
.
Their tensorial product is the tensor
T_{1}\otimes T_{2}\in{\cal L}^{r_{1}+r_{2}}_{s_{1}+s_{2}}(E)

defined by

(T_{1}\otimes T_{2})(\ell_{1,1},...,\ell_{2,1},...,{\vec{u}}_{1,1},...,{\vec{u}}_{2,1},...):=T_{1}(\ell_{1,1},...,{\vec{u}}_{1,1},...)T_{2}(\ell_{2,1},...,{\vec{u}}_{2,1},...).

(Q.18)

Particular case: with
\lambda\in{\cal L}^{0}_{0}(E)=\mathbb{R}
and
T\in{\cal L}^{r}_{s}(E)
,

\lambda\otimes T=T\otimes\lambda:=\lambda T\in{\cal L}^{r}_{s}(E).

(Q.19)

Example Q.1

let
T_{1},T_{2}\in{\cal L}^{1}_{1}(E)
. Quantification:
Let
T_{1}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}(T_{1})^{i}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
and
let
T_{2}={\sum_{k,m=1}^{n}}(T_{2})^{k}_{m}{\vec{e}}_{k}\otimes e^{m}
; Then

T_{1}\otimes T_{2}={\sum_{i,j,k,m=1}^{n}}(T_{1})^{i}_{k}(T_{2})^{j}_{m}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes{\vec{e}}_{j}\otimes e^{k}\otimes e^{m}\in{\cal L}^{2}_{2}(E)
.

Remark Q.2

Alternative definition:

T_{1}\widetilde{\otimes}T_{2}:={\sum_{i,j,k,m=1}^{n}}(T_{1})^{i}_{j}(T_{2})^{k}_{m}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}\otimes{\vec{e}}_{k}\otimes e^{m}\in{\cal L}({E^{*}},E,{E^{*}},E;\mathbb{R})
.
And we get back to the previous definition thanks to the natural canonical isomorphism

\widetilde{J}:{\cal L}({E^{*}},E,{E^{*}},E;\mathbb{R})\rightarrow{\cal L}({E^{*}},{E^{*}},E,E;\mathbb{R})={\cal L}^{2}_{2}(E)

defined by

\widetilde{J}(\widetilde{T})=T
where
T(\ell,m,{\vec{v}},{\vec{w}})=\widetilde{T}(\ell,{\vec{v}},m,{\vec{w}})
.

Q.5
Contractions

Q.5.1
Contraction of a linear form with a vector

Let
\ell\in{\cal L}^{0}_{1}(E)={E^{*}}
and
{\vec{w}}\in E
.
Their contraction is the value

\ell({\vec{w}})\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize linearity}}\ell.{\vec{w}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{w}}.\ell.

(Q.20)

And with a basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
and its dual basis
(\pi_{ei})
,
\ell={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\ell_{i}\pi_{ei}
and
{\vec{w}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}w_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
give

\ell.{\vec{w}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\ell_{i}w_{i}=[\ell]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}w_{i}\ell_{i}={\vec{w}}.\ell={\rm Tr}({\vec{w}}\otimes\ell),

(Q.21)

where
{\rm Tr}
is the objective trace operator
{\rm Tr}:{\cal L}(E;E)\simeq{\cal L}^{1}_{1}(E)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

(defined by
{\rm Tr}({\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes\pi_{ej})=\delta^{i}_{j}
).
Duality notations:
\ell.{\vec{w}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\ell_{i}w^{i}
, and Einstein convention is satisfied.

Exercice Q.3

Use the change of coordinate formulas to prove that the computation
\ell.{\vec{w}}
in (
Q.21
)
gives a result independent of the basis.

Answer
. Let
P
be the change of basis matrix.
So
[{\vec{w}}]_{new}=P^{-1}.[{\vec{w}}]_{old}
and
[\ell]_{new}=[\ell]_{old}.P
, cf. (
A.28
),
thus
[\ell]_{new}.[{\vec{w}}]_{new}=([\ell]_{old}.P).(P^{-1}.[{\vec{w}}]_{old})=[\ell]_{old}.(P.P^{-1}).[{\vec{w}}]_{old}=[\ell]_{old}[{\vec{w}}]_{old}
(
=\ell.{\vec{w}}
).

Q.5.2
Contraction of a
{1\choose 1}
tensor and a vector

Let
\ell\in E^{*}
and
{\vec{u}}\in E
.
The contraction of the elementary tensor
{\vec{w}}\otimes\ell\in{\cal L}^{1}_{1}(E)
with
{\vec{u}}
is defined by:

({\vec{w}}\otimes\underbrace{\ell).{\vec{u}}}_{\makebox[2.84544pt]{\footnotesize\rm contraction}}=(\ell.{\vec{u}}){\vec{w}}.

(Q.22)

Thus, if
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is a basis in
E
and
(\pi_{ei})
is the dual basis,
and
T={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}T_{ij}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes\pi_{ej}\in{\cal L}^{1}_{1}(E)
and
{\vec{u}}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}u_{j}{\vec{e}}_{j}\in E
, then

T={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}T_{ij}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad T.{\vec{u}}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}T_{ij}u_{j}^{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}

(Q.23)

because
\pi_{ej}({\vec{u}})=u_{j}
. Duality notations:
T.{\vec{u}}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}T^{i}_{j}u^{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}
.

Then, with the natural canonical isomorphism (
{\cal L}^{1}_{1}(E)=
)
{\cal L}(E,E^{*};\mathbb{R})\simeq{\cal L}(E;E)
, see (
T.7
),
any endomorphism
L\in{\cal L}(E;E)
defined by
L.{\vec{e}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}L_{ij}{\vec{e}}_{i}
can be written, for calculation purpose,

{\widetilde{L}}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}L_{ij}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes\pi_{ej}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}L,\quad\hbox{which means}\quad L.{\vec{u}}\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqobjcL})}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}L_{ij}u_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}

(Q.24)

when
{\vec{u}}=\sum_{i}u_{j}{\vec{e}}_{j}
,
since
\pi_{ej}({\vec{u}})=u_{j}
. Duality notations:
L={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}{L^{i}{}_{j}}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
.

Q.5.3
Contractions of uniform tensors

More generally, the contraction of two tensors, if meaningful, is defined thanks to (
Q.20
):
Let
T_{1}\in{\cal L}^{r_{1}}_{s_{1}}(E)
,
T_{2}\in{\cal L}^{r_{2}}_{s_{2}}(E)
,

\ell\in{E^{*}}
and
{\vec{u}}\in E
.

Definition Q.4

The objective contraction of
T_{1}\otimes\ell\in{\cal L}^{r_{2}}_{s_{2}+1}(E)

and
{\vec{u}}\otimes T_{2}\in{\cal L}^{r_{2}+1}_{s_{2}}(E)
is the tensor

(T_{1}\otimes\ell).({\vec{u}}\otimes T_{2})\in{\cal L}^{r_{1}+r_{2}}_{s_{1}+s_{2}}
given by

(T_{1}\otimes\underbrace{\ell).({\vec{u}}}_{\makebox[2.84544pt]{\footnotesize\rm contraction}}\otimes T_{2}):=(\ell.{\vec{u}})\,T_{1}\otimes T_{2}.

(Q.25)

In particular
(T_{1}\otimes\ell).{\vec{u}}=(\ell.{\vec{u}})\,T_{1}
(as in (
Q.22
)),
and
\ell.({\vec{u}}\otimes T_{2})=(\ell.{\vec{u}})\,T_{2}
.

And the objective contraction of
T_{1}\otimes{\vec{u}}\in{\cal L}^{r_{2}+1}_{s_{2}}(E)

and
\ell\otimes T_{2}\in{\cal L}^{r_{2}}_{s_{2}+1}(E)
is the tensor

(T_{1}\otimes{\vec{u}}).(\ell\otimes T_{2})\in{\cal L}^{r_{1}+r_{2}}_{s_{1}+s_{2}}
given by

(T_{1}\otimes{\vec{u}}).(\ell\otimes T_{2})=({\vec{u}}.\ell)\,T_{1}\otimes T_{2}\quad(=(\ell.{\vec{u}})\,T_{1}\otimes T_{2}).

(Q.26)

Quantification with a basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
, examples to avoid cumbersome notations:

Example Q.5

Let
T\in{\cal L}^{1}_{1}(E)={\cal L}^{1}_{0+1}(E)
,
T={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}T^{i}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
.
With
{\vec{w}}\in E\sim{E^{**}}={\cal L}^{1}_{0}(E)
,
{\vec{w}}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}w^{j}{\vec{e}}_{j}
,
(
Q.25
) gives
T.{\vec{w}}\in{\cal L}^{1}_{0}(E)\sim E
and

T.{\vec{w}}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}T^{i}_{j}w^{j}{\vec{e}}_{i},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad[T.{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[T]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}\quad\hbox{(column matrix)}.

(Q.27)

(Einstein’s convention is satisfied.)
Indeed,
T.{\vec{w}}={\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}}T^{i}_{j}w^{k}({\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}).{\vec{e}}_{k}={\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}}T^{i}_{j}w^{k}{\vec{e}}_{i}(e^{j}.{\vec{e}}_{k})={\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}}T^{i}_{j}w^{k}{\vec{e}}_{i}(\delta^{j}_{k})={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}T^{i}_{j}w^{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}
.
With
\ell\in{E^{*}}={\cal L}^{0}_{1}(E)
,
\ell={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\ell_{i}e^{i}
,
(
Q.25
) gives
\ell.T\in{\cal L}^{0}_{1}(E)={E^{*}}
and

\ell.T={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}\ell_{i}T^{i}_{j}e^{j},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad[\ell.T]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[\ell]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[T]_{|{\vec{e}}}\quad\hbox{(row matrix)}.

(Q.28)

(Einstein’s convention is satisfied.)
Indeed
\ell.T=({\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\ell_{i}e^{i}).({\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}}T^{k}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{k}\otimes e^{j})={\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}}\ell_{i}T^{k}_{j}(e^{i}.{\vec{e}}_{k})e^{j}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}\ell_{i}T^{i}_{j}e^{j}
.

Example Q.6

Let
S,T\in{\cal L}^{1}_{1}(E)
,
S={\sum_{i,k=1}^{n}}S^{i}_{k}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{k}

and
T={\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}}T^{k}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{k}\otimes e^{j}
. Then

S.T={\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}}S^{i}_{k}T^{k}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad[S.T]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[S]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[T]_{|{\vec{e}}}

(Q.29)

(Einstein’s convention is satisfied.)
Indeed

S.T=({\sum_{i,k=1}^{n}}S^{i}_{k}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{k}).(\sum_{j,m=1}^{n}T^{m}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{m}\otimes e^{j})={\sum_{i,j,k,m=1}^{n}}S^{i}_{k}T^{m}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}(e^{k}.{\vec{e}}_{m})\otimes e^{j}={\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}}S^{i}_{k}T^{k}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
.

Example Q.7

Let
T\in{\cal L}^{1}_{2}(E)
,
T={\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}}T^{i}_{jk}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}\otimes e^{k}
,
and
{\vec{u}},{\vec{w}}\in E\sim{\cal L}^{1}_{0}(E)
,
{\vec{w}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}w^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
and
{\vec{u}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}u^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
. Then

T.{\vec{w}}={\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}}T^{i}_{jk}w^{k}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}\in{\cal L}^{1}_{1}(E),\quad\hbox{and}\quad(T.{\vec{w}}).{\vec{u}}={\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}}T^{i}_{jk}w^{k}u^{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}T({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}}).

(Q.30)

(Einstein’s convention is satisfied.)
So
[T.{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[{\sum_{k=1}^{n}}T^{i}_{jk}w^{k}]_{i=1,...,n\atop j=1,...,n}
.
And with
\ell\in{E^{*}}
,
\ell={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\ell_{i}e^{i}
,

((T.{\vec{w}}).{\vec{u}}).\ell={\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}}T^{i}_{jk}w^{k}u^{j}\ell_{i}=T(\ell,{\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})=\ell.T({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})=\ell.(T.{\vec{w}}).{\vec{u}}.

(Q.31)

Q.5.4
Objective double contractions of uniform tensors

Definition Q.8

Let
S,T\in{\cal L}^{1}_{1}(E)
. And let
({\vec{e}}_{i})
be a basis in
E
,
(e^{i})
its dual basis,
S={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}S^{i}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
and
T={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}T^{i}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
.
The double objective contraction
S\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}T
of
S
and
T
is defined by

S\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}T={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}S^{i}_{j}T^{j}_{i}.

(Q.32)

(Einstein convention is satisfied.)

Proposition Q.9

S\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}T
defined in (
Q.32
) is an invariant:
It is the trace
{\rm Tr}(L_{S}\circ L_{T})
of the endomorphisms
L_{S},L_{T}\in{\cal L}(E;E)
naturally canonically associated
to
S
and
T
(given by
\ell.L_{S}.{\vec{u}}:=S(\ell,{\vec{u}})
and
\ell.L_{T}.{\vec{u}}:=T(\ell,{\vec{u}})
for all
({\vec{u}},\ell)\in E\times E^{*}
). So the real value
{\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}S^{i}_{j}T^{j}_{i}
has the same real value regardless of the chosen basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
.
(Which is not the case of the term to term matrix multiplication
S:T={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}S^{i}_{j}T^{i}_{j}
, see next §
Q.5.5
and example
Q.13
.)

Proof.
Let
({\vec{a}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
be two bases
and
P=[P^{i}_{j}]
be the transition matrix from
({\vec{a}}_{i})
to
({\vec{b}}_{i})
, i.e.,
{\vec{b}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}P^{i}_{j}{\vec{a}}_{i}
for all
j
.
Let
Q=[Q^{i}_{j}]:=P^{-1}
.
Then
b^{i}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}Q^{i}_{j}a^{i}
.
Let
S=\sum_{ij}(S_{a})^{i}_{j}{\vec{a}}_{i}\otimes a^{j}=\sum_{ij}(S_{b})^{i}_{j}{\vec{b}}_{i}\otimes b^{j}
.
So
[(S_{b})^{i}_{j}]=P^{-1}.[(S_{a})^{i}_{j}].P
(change of basis formula for
{1\choose 1}
tensors identified with endomorphisms), i.e.
(S_{b})^{i}_{j}=\sum_{km}Q^{i}_{k}(S_{a})^{k}_{m}P^{m}_{j}
for all
i,j
.
Idem with
T
.
Thus

\sum_{i,j}(S_{b})^{i}_{j}(T_{b})^{j}_{i}=\sum_{i,j,k,m,\alpha,\beta}Q^{i}_{k}(S_{a})^{k}_{m}P^{m}_{j}Q^{j}_{\alpha}(T_{a})^{\alpha}_{\beta}P^{\beta}_{i}=\sum_{i,j,k,m,\alpha,\beta}(S_{a})^{k}_{m}(T_{a})^{\alpha}_{\beta}P^{\beta}_{i}Q^{i}_{k}P^{m}_{j}Q^{j}_{\alpha}=\sum_{k,m,\alpha,\beta}(S_{a})^{k}_{m}(T_{a})^{\alpha}_{\beta}\delta^{\beta}_{k}\delta^{m}_{\alpha}=\sum_{k,m}(S_{a})^{k}_{m}(T_{a})^{m}_{k}
.

Definition Q.10

More generally, the objective double contractions
S\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}T
of uniform tensors,
is obtained by applying the objective simple contraction twice consecutively, when applicable.

E.g.,
T_{1}\otimes\ell_{1,1}\otimes\ell_{1,2}
and

{\vec{u}}_{2,1}\otimes{\vec{u}}_{2,2}\otimes T_{2}
give

\displaystyle{(T_{1}\otimes\ell_{1,1}\otimes\underbrace{\ell_{1,2}).({\vec{u}}_{2,1}}_{\hbox{\footnotesize first}}\otimes{\vec{u}}_{2,2}\otimes T_{2})=}

\displaystyle{{}(\ell_{1,2}.{\vec{u}}_{2,1})(T_{1}\otimes\underbrace{\ell_{1,1})\otimes({\vec{u}}_{2,2}}_{\hbox{\footnotesize second}}\otimes T_{2})}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}(\ell_{1,2}.{\vec{u}}_{2,1})(\ell_{1,1}.{\vec{u}}_{2,2})\,T_{1}\otimes T_{2}.}

(Q.33)

Example Q.11

Let
S\in{\cal L}^{1}_{2}(E)
,
T\in{\cal L}^{2}_{1}(E)
,

S={\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}}S^{i}_{jk}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}\otimes e^{j}
,

T=\sum_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma=1}^{n}T^{\alpha\beta}_{\gamma}{\vec{e}}_{\alpha}\otimes{\vec{e}}_{\beta}\otimes e^{\gamma}
.
Then

S.T=\sum_{i,j,k,\beta,\gamma=1}^{n}S^{i}_{jk}T^{k\beta}_{\gamma}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}\otimes{\vec{e}}_{\beta}\otimes e^{\gamma},\quad\hbox{and}\quad S\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}T=\sum_{i,j,k,\gamma=1}^{n}S^{i}_{jk}T^{kj}_{\gamma}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{\gamma}.

(Q.34)

(Einstein’s convention is satisfied.)

Exercice Q.12

If
S\in{\cal L}(E,F;\mathbb{R})
,
T\in{\cal L}(F,G;\mathbb{R})
and
U\in{\cal L}(G,E;\mathbb{R})
then prove

S\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}(T.U)=(S.T)\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}U=(U.S)\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}T\quad\hbox{(circular permutation)}.

(Q.35)

Answer
. If
S=\sum S^{i}_{j}{\vec{a}}_{i}\otimes b^{j}
,

T=\sum T^{i}_{j}{\vec{b}}_{i}\otimes c^{j}

and
U=\sum U^{i}_{j}{\vec{c}}_{i}\otimes a^{j}
,
then
T.U=\sum T^{i}_{k}U^{k}_{j}{\vec{b}}_{i}\otimes a^{j}
,
thus
S\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to3.87498pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}(T.U)=\sum S^{i}_{m}T^{m}_{k}U^{k}_{i}
,
and

S.T=\sum S^{i}_{k}T^{k}_{j}{\vec{a}}_{i}\otimes c^{j}
,
so
(S.T)\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to3.87498pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}U=\sum S^{i}_{k}T^{k}_{m}U^{m}_{i}
.
And the second equality thanks to the symmetry of
\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to3.87498pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}
, i.e.
(S.T)\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to3.87498pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}U=U\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to3.87498pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}(S.T)=(U.S)\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to3.87498pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}T

with the previous calculation.

We define in the same way the triple objective contraction (apply the simple contraction three times consecutively).
E.g., with (
Q.34
) we get

S\mathop{\;\raise-1.65pt\hbox{\Large$0$}\mkern-10.2mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to5.59721pt{\hbox{.}\vss\hbox{.}\vss\hbox{.}}}}\;T=\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}S^{i}{}_{jk}T^{kj}{}_{i}.

(Q.36)

(Einstein’s convention is satisfied.)

Q.5.5
Non objective double contraction: Double matrix contraction

The engineers often use the double matrix contraction of second order tensors defined by (term to term multiplication):
If
S=[S_{ij}]=[S^{i}_{j}]
and
T=[T_{ij}]=[T^{i}_{j}]
then

S:T:={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}S_{ij}T_{ij}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}S^{i}_{j}T^{i}_{j}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\rm Tr}(S.T^{T}).

(Q.37)

Einstein’s convention is
not
satisfied, and the result is observer dependent for associated endomorphism:

Example Q.13

Let
({\vec{e}}_{i})
be a basis, let
S\in{\cal L}(E;E)
given by
[S]_{{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{0&4\cr 2&0}

(so
S.{\vec{e}}_{1}=2{\vec{e}}_{2}
and
S.{\vec{e}}_{2}=4{\vec{e}}_{1}
). Then the double matrix contraction (
Q.37
) gives

S:S=[S]_{{\vec{e}}}:[S]_{{\vec{e}}}=4*4+2*2=20.

(Q.38)

Change of basis: let
{\vec{b}}_{1}={\vec{e}}_{1}
and
{\vec{b}}_{2}=2{\vec{e}}_{2}
.
The transition matrix from
({\vec{e}}_{i})
to
({\vec{b}}_{i})
is
P=\pmatrix{1&0\cr 0&2}
.
Thus
[S]_{{\vec{b}}}=P^{-1}.[S]_{{\vec{e}}}.P=\pmatrix{1&0\cr 0&{1\over 2}}.\pmatrix{0&8\cr 2&0}=\pmatrix{0&8\cr 1&0}
. Thus

S:S=[S]_{{\vec{b}}}:[S]_{{\vec{b}}}=8*8+1*1=65\neq 20.

(Q.39)

To be compared with the double objective contraction:

[S]_{{\vec{e}}}\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}[S]_{{\vec{e}}}=4*2+2*4=16=[S]_{{\vec{b}}}\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}[S]_{{\vec{b}}}=S\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}S
(observer independent result = objective result).

So it is absurd to use
S:S
(double matrix contraction) if you need objectivity:
Recall that the foot is the international vertical unit in aviation, and thus the use of the double
objective
contraction is vital, while the use of the double matrix contraction can be fatal (really).
Also see the Mars climate orbiter probe crash.

Exercice Q.14

Let
S\in{\cal L}^{0}_{2}(E)
(e.g. a metric), let
({\vec{a}}_{i})
be a Euclidean basis in foot,
and let
({\vec{b}}_{i})=(\lambda{\vec{a}}_{i})
be the related euclidean basis in metre (change of unit).
Give
[S]_{|{\vec{a}}}:[S]_{|{\vec{a}}}
and
[S]_{|{\vec{b}}}:[S]_{|{\vec{b}}}
and compare.
(The simple and double objective contractions are impossible here since
S
and
T
are not compatible.)

Answer
. Let
S={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}S_{a,ij}a^{i}\otimes a^{j}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}S_{b,ij}b^{i}\otimes b^{j}
.
Since
({\vec{b}}_{i})=(\lambda{\vec{a}}_{i})
we have
b^{i}={1\over\lambda}a^{i}
.
Thus
{\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}S_{a,ij}a^{i}\otimes a^{j}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}S_{a,ij}\lambda^{2}b^{i}\otimes b^{j}
,
thus
\lambda^{2}S_{a,ij}=S_{b,ij}
.
Thus

[S]_{|{\vec{b}}}:[S]_{|{\vec{b}}}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}(S_{b,ij})^{2}=\lambda^{4}{\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}(S_{a,ij})^{2}=\lambda^{4}[S]_{|{\vec{a}}}:[S]_{|{\vec{a}}},

(Q.40)

with
\lambda^{4}\geq 100
: Quite a difference isn’t it?

Q.6
Kronecker (contraction) tensor, trace

Definition Q.15

The Kronecker tensor is the
{1\choose 1}
uniform tensor
{\underline{\underline{\delta}}}\in{\cal L}^{1}_{1}(E)
defined by

\forall(\ell,{\vec{u}})\in{E^{*}}\times E,\quad{\underline{\underline{\delta}}}(\ell,{\vec{u}}):=\ell.{\vec{u}}.

(Q.41)

And the Kronecker symbols relative to a basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
are the reals defined by, calling
(\pi_{ei})
the dual basis,

\delta_{ij}:=\delta(\pi_{ei},{\vec{e}}_{j})=\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&1\;\hbox{if }i=j,\cr&0\;\hbox{if }i\neq j,\cr}}\,\right\}\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad{\underline{\underline{\delta}}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{ei}\otimes e^{i},\quad[{\underline{\underline{\delta}}}]=[\delta_{j}]=[I]

(Q.42)

(identity matrix whatever the basis).
Duality notations:
\delta^{i}_{j}:=\delta(e^{i},{\vec{e}}_{j})
,
{\underline{\underline{\delta}}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{i}
and
[{\underline{\underline{\delta}}}]=[\delta^{i}_{j}]
.

Definition Q.16

The trace of a
{1\choose 1}
uniform tensor
T\in{\cal L}^{1}_{1}(E)
is

\widetilde{\rm Tr}(T)={\underline{\underline{\delta}}}\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}T\quad(={\rm Tr}(L_{T}))

(Q.43)

(with the natural canonical isomorphism
T\in{\cal L}^{1}_{1}(E)\simeq L_{T}\in{\cal L}(E;E)
given by
T(\ell,{\vec{v}}):=\ell.L_{T}.{\vec{v}}
).

Thus
\widetilde{\rm Tr}(T)={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}T^{i}{}_{i}
.

In particular
\widetilde{\rm Tr}({\underline{\underline{\delta}}})=n
,
and
\widetilde{\rm Tr}({\vec{v}}\otimes\ell)=\sum_{i}v^{i}\ell_{i}=\ell.{\vec{v}}
when

{\vec{v}}=\sum_{i}v^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
and
\ell=\sum_{j}\ell_{j}e^{j}
.

Appendix R
Tensors in
{T^{r}_{s}(U)}

R.1
Introduction, module, derivation

Let
A
and
B
be any sets, and let
{\cal F}(A;B)
be the set of functions
A\rightarrow B
.
The “plus” inner operation and the “dot” outer operation are defined by,
for all
f,g\in{\cal F}(A;B)
, all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and all
p\in A
,

\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&(f+g)(p):=f(p)+g(p),\quad\hbox{and}\quad\cr&(\lambda.f)(p):=\lambda\,f(p),\quad\lambda.f{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\lambda f.\crcr}}\,\right.

(R.1)

({\cal F}(A;B),+,.,\mathbb{R})
is thus a vector space on the field
\mathbb{R}
(see any elementary course) called
{\cal F}(A;B)
.

But the field
\mathbb{R}
is “too small” to define a tensor which can be seen as “a linear tool that satisfies the change of coordinate system rules”:

Example R.1

Fundamental counter-example: Derivation.
Let
U
be an open set in
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
.
The derivation
d:{\vec{w}}\in C^{1}(U;{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})\rightarrow d{\vec{w}}\in C^{0}(U;{\cal L}({\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}};{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}))
is
\mathbb{R}
-linear: In particular
d(\lambda{\vec{w}})=\lambda(d{\vec{w}})
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
…

…but
d
doesn’t satisfy the change of coordinate system rules, see (
S.35
).

So a derivation it
not
a tensor (it is a “spray”, see Abraham–Marsden
[
3
]
).

In fact, one requirement for
T
to be a tensor is, e.g. with
T={\vec{w}}
a vector field: For all
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(U;\mathbb{R})
, and all
{\vec{w}}\in\Gamma(U)
(
C^{\infty}
-vector field),

T(\varphi{\vec{w}})=\varphi\,T({\vec{w}}).

(R.2)

While

d(\varphi{\vec{w}})\neq\varphi\,d({\vec{w}}),\quad\hbox{because}\quad d(\varphi{\vec{w}})=\varphi\,d{\vec{w}}+d\varphi.{\vec{w}}.

(R.3)

Thus the elementary
\mathbb{R}
-linearity requirement “
T.(\lambda{\vec{w}})=\lambda(T.{\vec{w}})
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}

is not sufficient to characterize a tensor: The
\mathbb{R}
-linearity has to be replaced by the
C^{\infty}(U;\mathbb{R})
-linearity, cf. (
R.2
).

Thus we will have to replace a real vector space
(V,+,.,\mathbb{R})
over the field
\mathbb{R}
with the “module”
(V,+,.,C^{\infty}(U;\mathbb{R}))
over the ring
C^{\infty}(U;\mathbb{R})
, which mainly amounts to consider (
R.1
) for all
\lambda=\varphi\in C^{\infty}(U;\mathbb{R})
.
Remark: The use of a module is very similar to the use of a vector space, but for the use of the inverse:
all real
\lambda\neq 0
has a multiplicative inverse in
\mathbb{R}
(namely
{1\over\lambda}
), but a function
f\in C^{\infty}(U;\mathbb{R})

s.t. “
f\neq 0
and
f
vanishes at one point” doesn’t have a multiplicative inverse in
C^{\infty}(U;\mathbb{R})
.

R.2
Field of functions and vector fields

Framework of classical mechanics:

U
is an open set in an affine space
{\cal E}
which associated vector is
E
.
And the definition of tensors is done at a fixed time
t
(concerns the space variables).
As before, the approach is first qualitative, then quantitative with a basis
({\vec{e}}_{i}(p))

and its dual basis
(\pi_{ei}(p))=(e^{i}(p))
,
at any
p\in{\cal E}
.

R.2.1
Field of functions

Let
f\in C^{\infty}(U;\mathbb{R})
be a function. The associated function field is

{\widetilde{f}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr U&\rightarrow U\times\mathbb{R}\cr p&\rightarrow{\widetilde{f}}(p):=(p;f(p)),\crcr}}\,\right.

(R.4)

and
p
is called the base point.
So
{\rm Im}{\widetilde{f}}=\{(p;f(p)):p\in U\}
is the graph of
f
.
Definition:

{T^{0}_{0}(U)}:=\{{\widetilde{f}}:f\in C^{\infty}(U;\mathbb{R})\}=\{\hbox{field of functions}\}=\hbox{the set of ${0\choose 0}$ type tensor on\penalty\ $U$},

(R.5)

or the set of tensors of order
0
on
U
.
Abusive short notations (to lighten the writings):

{\widetilde{f}}(p){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}f(p),\quad\hbox{and}\quad T^{0}_{0}(U){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}C^{\infty}(U;\mathbb{R}),

(R.6)

but keep the base point in mind (no ubiquity gift).

In
{T^{0}_{0}(U)}
,
the internal sum is defined by, for all
{\widetilde{f}},{\widetilde{g}}\in{T^{0}_{0}(U)}
with
{\widetilde{f}}(p)=(p;f(p))
and
{\widetilde{g}}(p)=(p;g(p))
,

({\widetilde{f}}+{\widetilde{g}})(p):=(p;(f+g)(p))\quad(=(p;f(p)+g(p))),

(R.7)

and the external multiplication on the ring
C^{\infty}(U;\mathbb{R})
is defined by, for all
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(U;\mathbb{R})
,

(\varphi{\widetilde{f}})(p):=(p;(\varphi f)(p))\quad(=(p;\varphi(p)f(p)))

(R.8)

(the base point
p
remains unchanged).
Thus
({T^{0}_{0}(U)},+,.)
is a module over the ring
C^{\infty}(U;\mathbb{R})
.

R.2.2
Vector fields

Let
{\vec{w}}\in C^{\infty}(U,E)
be a vector valued function
(at least Lipschitzian, to get integral curves, cf. Cauchy–Lipschitz theorem).
The associated vector field is

{\widetilde{\vec{w}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr U&\rightarrow U\times E\cr p&\rightarrow{\widetilde{\vec{w}}}(p)=(p;{\vec{w}}(p)).\crcr}}\,\right.

(R.9)

So
{\rm Im}{\widetilde{\vec{w}}}=\{(p;{\vec{w}}(p)):p\in U\}
is the graph of
{\vec{w}}
,
and the definition of
{\widetilde{\vec{w}}}
tells that the vector
{\vec{w}}(p)
has to be drawn at
p
(the base point).
Abusive short notation:

{\widetilde{\vec{w}}}(p){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{w}}(p)\quad\hbox{instead of }{\widetilde{\vec{w}}}(p)=(p;{\vec{w}}(p)).

(R.10)

It lightens the notations, but keep the base point in mind.
Let

\Gamma(U):=\hbox{ the set of vector fields on\penalty\ $U$}.

(R.11)

More precisely,
we will use the following full definition of vector fields
(see e.g. Abraham–Marsden
[
3
]
):
A vector field is built from tangent vectors to curves.
It makes sense on non planar surfaces, and more generally on differential manifolds.

R.3
Differential forms

The basic concept is that of vector fields.
A first over-layer is made of differential forms (which “measure vector fields”):

Definition R.2

Let
\alpha\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr U&\rightarrow E^{*}\cr p&\rightarrow\alpha(p)\cr}}\,\right\}

(so
\alpha(p)
is a linear form at
p
).
The associated differential form (also called a
1
-form) is “the field of linear forms” defined by

{\widetilde{\alpha}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr U&\rightarrow U\times{E^{*}}\cr p&\rightarrow{\widetilde{\alpha}}(p)=(p;\alpha(p))\quad\hbox{( = ``a pointed linear form at $p$'')}.\crcr}}\,\right.

(R.12)

And
p
is called the base point, and
{\rm Im}{\widetilde{\alpha}}=\{(p;\alpha(p)):p\in U\}
is the graph of
\alpha
.

Thus, if
{\widetilde{\alpha}}\in\Omega^{1}(U)
(differential form) and
{\widetilde{\vec{w}}}\in\Gamma(U)
(vector field), then
