{\widetilde{\alpha}}.{\widetilde{\vec{w}}}\in{T^{0}_{0}(U)}
(field of scalar valued functions) satisfies

{\widetilde{\alpha}}.{\widetilde{\vec{w}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr U&\rightarrow U\times\mathbb{R}\cr p&\rightarrow({\widetilde{\alpha}}.{\widetilde{\vec{w}}})(p)=(p;(\alpha.{\vec{w}})(p))=(p;\alpha(p).{\vec{w}}(p))\;\in U\times\mathbb{R}.\crcr}}\,\right.

(R.13)

Short notation:

{\widetilde{\alpha}}(p){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\alpha(p),\quad\hbox{instead of }{\widetilde{\alpha}}(p)=(p;\alpha(p)),

(R.14)

but keep the base point in mind. And

\Omega^{1}(U):=\hbox{ the set of differential forms\penalty\ $U$}.

(R.15)

R.4
Tensors

A second over-layer is introduced with the tensors with are “functions defined on vector fields and on differential forms” (which “measure vector fields and differential forms”).

Let
r,s\in\mathbb{N}
,
r{+}s\geq 1
, and let
T:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr U&\rightarrow{\cal L}^{r}_{s}(E)\cr p&\rightarrow T(p)\crcr}}\,\right\}

(so
T(p)
is a uniform
{r\choose s}
tensor for each
p
, cf. (
Q.3.1
)).
And consider the associated function

{\widetilde{T}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr U&\rightarrow U\times{\cal L}^{r}_{s}(E)\cr p&\rightarrow{\widetilde{T}}(p)=(p;T(p))\crcr}}\,\right.

(R.16)

Abusive short notation:

{\widetilde{T}}(p){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}T(p)\quad\hbox{instead of }{\widetilde{T}}(p)=(p;T(p)),

(R.17)

but keep the base point in mind.

Definition R.3

(Abraham–Marsden
[
3
]
.)

{\widetilde{T}}
is a tensor of type
{r\choose s}
iff
T
is
C^{\infty}(U;\mathbb{R})
-multilinear (not only
\mathbb{R}
-multilinear), i.e.,
for all
f\in C^{\infty}(U;\mathbb{R})
, all
z_{1},z_{2}
vector field or differentiable form where applicable, and all
p\in U
,

\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&T(p)(...,z_{1}(p)+z_{2}(p),...)=T(p)(...,z_{1}(p),...)+T(p)(...,z_{2}(p),...),\quad\hbox{and}\quad\cr&T(p)(...,f(p)z_{1}(p),...)=f(p)\,T(p)(...,z_{1}(p),...),\cr}}\,\right.

(R.18)

written in short

\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr&T(...,z_{1}+z_{2},...)=T(...,z_{1},...)+T(...,z_{2},...),\quad\hbox{and}\quad\cr&T(...,fz_{1},...)=f\,T(...,z_{1},...).\cr}}\,\right.

(R.19)

And

{T^{r}_{s}(U)}:=\hbox{ the set of ${r\choose s}$ type tensors on\penalty\ $U$}.

(R.20)

(Recall:
\displaystyle{T^{0}_{0}(U)}:=C^{\infty}(U;\mathbb{R})
the set of function fields, cf. (
R.4
).)

Remark R.4

Definition in differential geometry lessons: A tensor is a section of a certain bundle over a manifold.
For classical mechanics, definition
R.3
gives an equivalent definition.

R.5
First Examples

R.5.1
Type
{0\choose 1}
tensor = differential forms

If
T\in{T^{0}_{1}(U)}
then
T(p)\in{E^{*}}
, so
T=\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(U)
is a differential form:
{T^{0}_{1}(U)}\subset\Omega^{1}(U)
.

Converse: Does a differential form
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(U)
defines a
{0\choose 1}
type tensor on
U
?
Yes: We have to check (
R.18
), which is trivial.
So
\alpha\in{T^{0}_{1}(U)}
, so
\Omega^{1}(U)\subset{T^{0}_{1}(U)}
.

Thus

{T^{0}_{1}(U)}=\Omega^{1}(U).

(R.21)

R.5.2
Type
{1\choose 0}
tensor (identified to a vector field)

Let
T\in{T^{0}_{1}(U)}
,
so
T(p)\in{\cal L}^{1}_{0}(E)={\cal L}(E^{*};\mathbb{R})={E^{**}}
for all
p\in U
.
Thus, thanks to the natural canonical isomorphism
{E^{**}}\simeq E
,
T(p)
can be identified to a vector,
thus
{T^{0}_{1}(U)}\subset\Gamma(U)
.

Converse: Does a vector field
{\vec{w}}\in\Gamma(U)
defines a
{1\choose 0}
type tensor on
U
?
Yes: We have to check (
R.18
), which is trivial.
So
\Gamma(U)\subset{T^{1}_{0}(U)}
.

Thus

{T^{1}_{0}(U)}\simeq\Gamma(U).

(R.22)

R.5.3
A metric is a
{0\choose 2}
tensor

Let
T\in{T^{0}_{2}(U)}
,
so
T(p)\in{\cal L}^{0}_{2}(E)
for all
p\in U
,
and
T({\vec{u}},{\vec{w}})\in{T^{0}_{0}(U)}
for all
{\vec{u}},{\vec{w}}\in\Gamma(U)
.

Definition R.5

A metric
g
on
U
is a
{0\choose 2}
type tensor on
U
such that, for all
p\in E
,

g(p){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}g_{p}
is an inner dot product on
E
.

R.6
{1\choose 1}
tensor, identification with fields of endomorphisms

Let
T\in{T^{1}_{1}(U)}
,
so
T(p)\in{\cal L}^{1}_{1}(E)
for all
p\in U
,
and
T(\alpha,{\vec{w}})\in{T^{0}_{0}(U)}
for all
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(U)
and
{\vec{w}}\in\Gamma(U)

(so
T(p)(\alpha(p),{\vec{w}}(p))\in\mathbb{R}
for all
p
).

The associated field of endomorphisms on
U
is

{\widetilde{L}}_{T}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr U&\rightarrow U\times{\cal L}(E;E)\cr p&\rightarrow{\widetilde{L}}_{T}(p)=(p,L_{T}(p))\crcr}}\,\right\}

where
L_{T}(p)
is identified with
T(p)
thanks to the natural canonical isomorphism

{\cal L}(E;E)\simeq{\cal L}(E^{*},E;\mathbb{R})={\cal L}^{1}_{1}(E)

given by

\forall\ell\in{E^{*}},\;\forall{\vec{w}}\in E,\;\quad\ell.(L_{T}(p).{\vec{w}})=T(p)(\ell,{\vec{w}}).

(R.23)

R.7
Unstationary tensor

Let
t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]\subset\mathbb{R}
.
Let
(T_{t})_{t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]}
be a family of
{r\choose s}
tensors, cf. (
R.16
).
Then
T:t\rightarrow T(t):=T_{t}
is called an unstationary tensor.
And the set of unstationary tensors is also noted
{T^{r}_{s}(U)}
.
E.g., a Eulerian velocity field is a
{1\choose 0}
unstationary vector field.

Appendix S
Differential, its eventual gradients, divergences

S.1
Differential

The definition of the differential of a function is observer independent: All observers have the same definition
(qualitative: no man made tool required, like a basis or an inner dot product).

S.1.1
Framework

Classical Framework:
{\cal E}
are
{\cal F}
affine spaces associated with vector spaces
E
and
F
, and
||.||_{E}
and
||.||_{F}
are norms in
E
and
F
such that
(E,||.||_{E})
and
(F,||.||_{F})
are complete (we need “limit that stay in the space as
h\rightarrow 0
”, ).

U
is an open set in
{\cal E}
, and
\Phi:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr U&\rightarrow{\cal F}\cr{p}&\rightarrow{p_{\!{\cal F}}}=\Phi({p})\crcr}}\,\right\}
is a function. If applicable,
{\cal E}
and/or
{\cal F}
can be replaced by
E
and/or
F
.
(The definitions can be generalized to manifolds.)
Reminder:

Definition S.1

Let
{p}\in U
. The function
\Phi
is said to be continuous at
p
iff
\displaystyle\Phi(q)\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{{q}\rightarrow p}\Phi({p})

relative to the considered norms, i.e.,
||\Phi(q)-\Phi(p)||_{F}\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{||q-p||_{E}\rightarrow 0}0
, also written (Landau notation): Near
p
,

\Phi({q})=\Phi({p})+o(1),

(S.1)

called “the zero-th order Taylor expansion of
\Phi
near
p
”.
In other words:

\forall\varepsilon>0
,
\exists\eta>0
s.t.
\forall{q}\in{\cal E}
s.t.
||{q}-{p}||_{E}<\eta
we have
||\Phi({q})-\Phi({p})||_{F}<\varepsilon
.

And
C^{0}(U;{\cal F})
is the set of functions that are continuous at all
{p}\in U
.

S.1.2
Directional derivative and differential (observer independent)

Let
{p}\in U
,
{\vec{u}}\in E
, and let
f:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\cal F}
defined by

f(h):=\Phi({p}+h{\vec{u}})

(S.2)

Definition S.2

The function
\Phi
is differentiable at
{p}
in the direction
{\vec{u}}
iff
f
is derivable at
0
,
i.e. iff the limit

f^{\prime}(0)=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{\Phi({p}+h{\vec{u}})-\Phi({p})\over h}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}d\Phi({p})({\vec{u}})
exists in
F
, i.e. iff, near
p
,

\Phi({p}+h{\vec{u}})=\Phi({p})+h\,d\Phi({p})({\vec{u}})+o(h),

(S.3)

equation called the first order Taylor expansion of
\Phi
at
{p}
in the direction
{\vec{u}}

(it is the first order Taylor expansion of
f
near
p
).

Then
d\Phi({p})({\vec{u}})
is called the directional derivative of
\Phi
at
{p}
in the direction
{\vec{u}}
.

And if, for all
{\vec{u}}\in E
,
d\Phi({p})({\vec{u}})
exists (in
F
) then
\Phi
is called Gâteaux differentiable at
{p}
.

Exercice S.3

Prove: If
\Phi
is Gâteaux differentiable at
{p}
then
d\Phi({p})
is homogeneous, i.e.,
d\Phi({p})(\lambda{\vec{u}})=\lambda\,d\Phi({p})({\vec{u}})
for all
{\vec{u}}\in E
and all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.

Answer
.
\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{\Phi({p}+h(\lambda{\vec{u}}))-\Phi({p})\over h}=\lambda\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{\Phi({p}+\lambda h{\vec{u}})-\Phi({p})\over\lambda h}=\lambda\lim_{k\rightarrow 0}{\Phi({p}+k{\vec{u}})-\Phi({p})\over k}
.

Definition S.4

If
\Phi
is Gateaux differentiable and if moreover
d\Phi(p)
is linear and continuous at
p
,
then
\Phi
is said to be differentiable at
p
(or Fréchet differentiable at
p
). So

\Phi({q})=\Phi({p})+h\,d\Phi({p}).\overrightarrow{{p}{q}}+o(||\overrightarrow{{p}{q}}||_{E}),

(S.4)

since then
d\Phi(p)({\vec{u}}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}d\Phi(p).{\vec{u}}
for all
{\vec{u}}\in E
(linearity).

And the affine function
{a\!f\!\!f}_{\!\!{p}}:{q}\rightarrow{a\!f\!\!f}_{\!\!{p}}({q}):=\Phi({p})+d\Phi({p}).\overrightarrow{{p}{q}}
is the affine approximation of
\Phi
at
{p}
.
(So, the graph of
{a\!f\!\!f}_{\!\!{p}}
is the tangent plane of
\Phi
at
{p}
.)

Definition S.5

\Phi:U\rightarrow{\cal F}
is said to be differentiable in
U
iff
\Phi
is differentiable at all
{p}\in U
.
Then its differential is the map

d\Phi:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr U&\rightarrow{\cal L}(E;F)\cr{p}&\rightarrow d\Phi({p}).\crcr}}\,\right.

(S.5)

And
C^{1}(U;{\cal F})
is the set of differentiable functions
\psi
such that
d\Phi\in C^{0}(U;{\cal L}(E;F))
.

And
C^{2}(U;{\cal F})
is the set of differentiable functions
\psi
such that
d\Phi\in C^{1}(U;{\cal L}(E;F))
.

…
And
C^{k}(U;{\cal F})
is the set of differentiable functions
\psi
such that
d\Phi\in C^{k-1}(U;{\cal L}(E;F))
….

Proposition S.6

The differentiation (or derivation) operator
d:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr C^{1}(U;{\cal F})&\rightarrow C^{0}(U;{\cal L}(E;F))\cr\Phi&\rightarrow d\Phi\cr}}\,\right\}

is
\mathbb{R}
-linear (“a derivation is linear”).

Proof.
d(\Phi+\lambda\Psi)({p}).{\vec{u}}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{(\Phi+\lambda\Psi)({p}{+}h{\vec{u}})-(\Phi+\lambda\Psi)({p})\over h}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{\Phi({p}{+}h{\vec{u}})-\Phi({p})+\lambda\Psi({p}{+}h{\vec{u}})-\lambda\Psi({p})\over h}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{\Phi({p}{+}h{\vec{u}})-\Phi({p})\over h}+\lambda\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{\Psi({p}{+}h{\vec{u}})-\Psi({p})\over h}=d\Phi({p}).{\vec{u}}+\lambda d\Psi({p}).{\vec{u}}=(d\Phi({p})+\lambda d\Psi({p})).{\vec{u}}
for all
p
and
{\vec{u}}
,
thus
d(\Phi+\lambda\Psi)=d\Phi+\lambda d\Psi
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and
\Phi,\Psi\in C^{1}(U;{\cal F})
.

Exercice S.7

Prove: if
f\in C^{1}(U;\mathbb{R})
(scalar values) and
\Phi\in C^{1}(U;{\cal F})
then, for all
{\vec{u}}\in E
,

d(f\Phi).{\vec{u}}=(df.{\vec{u}})\Phi+f(d\Phi.{\vec{u}})

(S.6)

(and we also write
d(f\Phi)=\Phi\otimes df+f\,d\Phi
for a use with contraction rules).

Answer
.

\displaystyle{d(f\Phi)({p}).{\vec{u}}=}

\displaystyle{{}\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{f({p}{+}h{\vec{u}})\Phi({p}{+}h{\vec{u}})-f({p})\Phi({p})\over h}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{f({p}{+}h{\vec{u}})\Phi({p}{+}h{\vec{u}})-f({p})\Phi({p}{+}h{\vec{u}})\over h}+{f({p})\Phi({p}{+}h{\vec{u}})-f({p})\Phi({p})\over h}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{f({p}{+}h{\vec{u}})-f({p})\over h}(\Phi({p})+o(1))+\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}f({p}){\Phi({p}{+}h{\vec{u}})-\Phi({p})\over h}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}(df({p}).{\vec{u}})\Phi({p})+f({p})(d\Phi({p}).{\vec{u}}).}

(S.7)

Tensorial writing:
d(f\Phi).{\vec{u}}=(\Phi\otimes df).{\vec{u}}+(f\,d\Phi).{\vec{u}}
,
thanks to the contraction rule which gives

(\Phi\otimes df).{\vec{u}}+(f\,d\Phi).{\vec{u}}=\Phi(df.{\vec{u}})+f(d\Phi.{\vec{u}})
.

Remark S.8

In differential geometry, the definition of a tangent map is defined by, with definition
S.4
:

T\Phi:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr U\times E&\rightarrow{\cal F}\times F\cr({p},{\vec{u}})&\rightarrow T\Phi({p},{\vec{u}})=(\Phi({p}),d\Phi({p}).{\vec{u}}).\cr}}\,\right.

(S.8)

The two points
{p}
(input) and
\Phi({p})
(output) are the base points, and
the two vectors
{\vec{u}}
(input) and
d\Phi({p}).{\vec{u}}
(output) are the initial vector and its push-forward by
\Phi
.

S.1.3
Notation for the second order Differential

Let
\Phi\in C^{2}(U;{\cal F})
;
Thus
d\Phi\in C^{1}(U;{\cal L}(E;F))
, thus
d(d\Phi)\in C^{0}(U;{\cal L}(E;{\cal L}(E;F)))
;
So, for
p\in U
and
{\vec{u}}\in E
, we have

d(d\Phi)({p}).{\vec{u}}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{d\Phi({p}+h{\vec{u}})-d\Phi({p})\over h}\;\in{\cal L}(E;F)
, and, with
{\vec{v}}\in E
we have
(d(d\Phi)({p}).{\vec{u}}).{\vec{v}}\in F
.

Definition S.9

The bilinear map
d^{2}\Phi({p})\in{\cal L}(E,E;F)
is defined by

d^{2}\Phi({p})({\vec{u}},{\vec{v}})=(d(d\Phi)({p}).{\vec{u}}).{\vec{v}},

(S.9)

thanks to the natural canonical isomorphism
L\in{\cal L}(E;{\cal L}(E;F))\leftrightarrow T_{L}\in{\cal L}(E,E;F)
given by

T_{L}({\vec{u}}_{1},{\vec{u}}_{2}):=(L.{\vec{u}}_{1}).{\vec{u}}_{2}
for all
{\vec{u}}_{1},{\vec{u}}_{2}\in E
; Thus
L{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}T_{L}
, thus
d(d\Phi){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}d^{2}\Phi({p})\in{\cal L}(E,E;F)
.

This gives the usual second order Taylor expansion
of
\Phi
(supposed
C^{2}
) near
p
in the direction
{\vec{u}}
:

\Phi({p}+h{\vec{u}})=\Phi({p})+h\,d\Phi({p}).{\vec{u}}+{h^{2}\over 2}\,d^{2}\Phi({p})({\vec{u}},{\vec{u}})+o(h^{2})

(S.10)

(=the second order Taylor expansion of
f:h\rightarrow f(h)=\Phi({p}+h{\vec{u}})
near
h=0
, cf. (
S.2
)).

And Schwarz’s theorem tells that
d^{2}\Phi(p)
is symmetric when
\Phi
is
C^{2}
, i.e.
d^{2}\Phi({p})({\vec{u}},{\vec{v}})=d^{2}\Phi({p})({\vec{v}},{\vec{u}})
.

S.2
A basis and the
j
-th partial derivative

Definition S.10

Let
\Phi\in C^{1}(U;{\cal F})
,
{\vec{u}}\in\Gamma(U)
(a vector field),
p\in U
.
The derivative of
\Phi
at
p
along
{\vec{u}}
is defined by

\partial_{\vec{u}}\Phi({p}):=d\Phi({p}).{\vec{u}}({p})\quad(=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{\Phi({p}+h{\vec{u}}({p}))-\Phi({p})\over h}\;\in F).

(S.11)

This defines the directional derivative operator along
{\vec{u}}
:

\partial_{\vec{u}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr C^{1}(U;{\cal F})&\rightarrow C^{0}(U;F)\cr\Phi&\rightarrow\partial_{\vec{u}}(\Phi):=d\Phi.{\vec{u}},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad\partial_{\vec{u}}(\Phi)({p}):=d\Phi({p}).{\vec{u}}({p}).\crcr}}\,\right.

(S.12)

(And
\partial_{\vec{u}}(\Phi)({p}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{u}}(\Phi)({p})
in differential geometry thanks to
E\simeq{E^{**}}
which gives
\partial_{\vec{u}}\simeq{\vec{u}}
.)

In particular, if
({\vec{e}}_{i}({p}))
is a basis at
{p}
, then the
j
-th partial derivative of
\Phi
at
{p}

is
\partial_{{\vec{e}}_{j}}\Phi({p}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\partial_{j}\Phi({p})
(the derivative along
{\vec{e}}_{j}
), and the
j
-th directional derivative operator is

\partial_{j}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr C^{1}(U;{\cal F})&\rightarrow C^{0}(U;F)\cr\Phi&\rightarrow\framebox{$\displaystyle\partial_{j}\Phi:=d\Phi.{\vec{e}}_{j}$},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad\partial_{j}(\Phi)({p}):=d\Phi({p}).{\vec{e}}_{j}({p}).\crcr}}\,\right.

(S.13)

(In differential geometry
\partial_{j}\Phi{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{e}}_{j}(\Phi)
, so
{\vec{e}}_{j}(\Phi)({p}):=d\Phi({p}).{\vec{e}}_{j}({p})
.)

S.3
Application 1: Scalar valued functions

S.3.1
Differential of a scalar valued function (objective)

Here
\displaystyle\Phi{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}f:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr U&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr p&\rightarrow f(p)\crcr}}\,\right\}

is a
C^{1}
scalar valued function,
so
df\in\Omega^{1}(U)\cap C^{0}(U;E^{*})
(a
C^{0}
differential form).
So
df(p)\in E^{*}
for all
p\in U
, and
df(p).{\vec{u}}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{f(p+h{\vec{u}})-f(p)\over h}\in\mathbb{R}
for all
{\vec{u}}\in E
.

Exercice S.11

Prove: If
f,g\in C^{1}(U;\mathbb{R})
then (derivative of a product)

d(fg)=(df)g+f(dg),

(S.14)

i.e.,
d(fg).{\vec{w}}=(df.{\vec{w}})g+f(dg.{\vec{w}})
for all
{\vec{w}}\in\Gamma(U)
.

Answer
.
\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{f(p{+}h{\vec{w}})g(p{+}h{\vec{w}})-f(p)g(p)\over h}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{f(p{+}h{\vec{w}})g(p{+}h{\vec{w}})-f(p)g(p{+}h{\vec{w}})\over h}+\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{f(p)g(p{+}h{\vec{w}})-f(p)g(p)\over h}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{f(p{+}h{\vec{w}})-f(p)\over h}(g(p)+o(1))+\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}f(p){g(p{+}h{\vec{w}})-g(p)\over h}
, calculation that only requires the first order (affine) approximation of
f
and
g
:
We get the same result as with the affine functions

f(x)=a_{0}+a_{1}x
and
g(x)=b_{0}+b_{1}x
, which give
(fg)(x)=a_{0}b_{0}+(a_{0}b_{1}{+}a_{1}b_{0})x+a_{1}b_{1}x^{2}
, and then

(fg)^{\prime}(x)=a_{0}b_{1}{+}a_{1}b_{0}+2a_{1}b_{1}x
, which is indeed equal to
(f^{\prime}g+fg^{\prime})(x)=a_{1}(b_{0}{+}b_{1}x)+(a_{0}{+}a_{1}x)b_{1}
.

S.3.2
Quantification

Let
({\vec{e}}_{i}(p))
be a basis at
p
. So

\partial_{j}f(p)\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqpajpsi})}}df(p).{\vec{e}}_{j}(p)
(
=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{f(p+h{\vec{e}}_{j}(p))-f(p)\over h}
),
and we write

\partial_{j}f(p){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}f_{|j}(p).

(S.15)

So, with
(\pi_{ei}(p))
the dual basis of the basis
({\vec{e}}_{i}(p))
,
and with
f_{|j}(p):=\pi_{ei}(p).df(p)
(
j
-th component of
df(p)
in the basis
(\pi_{ei}(p))
),
we have

df={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}f_{|j}\pi_{ej},\quad\hbox{and}\quad[df(p)]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{f_{|1}(p)&...&f_{|n}(p)}\quad\hbox{(row matrix)}.

(S.16)

So
df.{\vec{u}}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}f_{|j}u_{j}=[df]_{|{\vec{e}}}.[{\vec{u}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}
when
{\vec{u}}(p)=\sum_{i}u_{i}(p){\vec{e}}_{i}(p)
.
In particular with a Cartesian basis,
(\pi_{ei}(p)){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}(dx_{j})
, and
df={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}{\partial f\over\partial x_{j}}dx_{j}
.

Duality notations:
\pi_{ei}=e^{i}
,
{\vec{u}}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}u^{j}{\vec{e}}_{j}
,
df={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}f_{|j}\,e^{j}
,
df.{\vec{u}}={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}f_{|j}u^{j}
, and with a Cartesian basis,
\pi_{ei}=dx^{i}
and
df={\sum_{j=1}^{n}}{\partial f\over\partial x^{j}}dx^{j}
.

Exercice S.12

Prove:
(fg)_{|j}=f_{|j}\,g+f\,g_{|j}
when
f,g:U\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
are
C^{1}
scalar valued functions.

Answer
. Apply (
S.7
): here
d(fg)=g\,df+f\,dg
, i.e.
d(fg).{\vec{e}}_{j}=(df.{\vec{e}}_{j})\,g+f\,(dg.{\vec{e}}_{j})
for all
j
.

And
df(p)\in E^{*}
satisfies the covariant change of basis formula for linear forms, i.e., if
({\vec{a}}_{i}(p))
and
({\vec{b}}_{i}(p))
are two bases at
p
and
P(p)
is the transition matrix from
({\vec{a}}_{i}(p))
to
({\vec{b}}_{i}(p))
, then

[df(p)]_{|{\vec{b}}}\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdefP1})}}[df(p)]_{|{\vec{a}}}.P(p)
, or in short:

[df]_{|{\vec{b}}}=[df]_{|{\vec{a}}}.P\quad(\hbox{covariance formula}).

(S.17)

S.3.3
Gradients (subjective) associated with a differential through inner dot products

Let
f\in C^{1}(U;\mathbb{R})
(a
C^{1}
scalar valued function). Choose (subjective) an inner dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
in
E
.

Definition S.13

The conjugate gradient
{\vec{\rm grad}}_{g}f(p)
of
f
at
p\in U
relative to
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
, also called the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-conjugate gradient of
f
at
p
,
is the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-Riesz representation vector of the linear form
df(p)\in E^{*}
:

{\vec{\rm grad}}_{g}f(p):={\vec{R}}_{g}(df(p)).

(S.18)

I.e., the vector
{\vec{\rm grad}}_{g}f(p)\in E
is characterized by, cf. (
F.2
),

\forall{\vec{u}}\in E,\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle df(p).{\vec{u}}=({\vec{\rm grad}}_{g}f(p),{\vec{u}})_{g}$}={\vec{\rm grad}}_{g}f(p)\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,\;{\vec{u}}.

(S.19)

Fundamental:
An English observer with his Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{a}
in foot and a French observer with his Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{b}
in metre
have the same differential
df
(defined independently of any unit of measurement); But do
not
have the same gradient:

{\vec{\rm grad}}_{b}f\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqrtr20})}}\lambda^{2}{\vec{\rm grad}}_{a}f\quad\hbox{with}\quad\lambda^{2}>10.

(S.20)

Quite different vectors isn’t it?
The “gradient vector” strongly depends on the chosen inner dot product. And to forget this fact leads to accidents like the crash of the Mars Climate Orbiter probe, cf. remark
A.14
.

Subjective first order Taylor expansion:

If an inner dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
exists and is used, then the first order Taylor expansion (
S.3
) gives

f(p+h{\vec{u}})=f(p)+h\,({\vec{\rm grad}}_{g}f(p),{\vec{u}})_{g}+o(h)\qquad(=f(p)+h\,{\vec{\rm grad}}_{g}f(p)\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}_{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize$\!\!\!g$}}}\,{\vec{u}}+o(h)).

(S.21)

Fundamental once again
(we insist):

\bullet
An inner dot product does not always exist (as a meaningful tool), see §
B.3.2
(thermodynamics),
thus, for a
C^{1}
function, a gradient does not always exists (contrary to a differential).

\bullet

df(p)
is a linear form (covariant) while
{\vec{\rm grad}}_{g}f(p)
is a vector (contravariant).
In particular the change of basis formulas differ, cf. (
A.28
):

[df]_{|{new}}=[df]_{|{old}}.P,\quad\hbox{while}\quad[{\vec{\rm grad}}_{g}]_{|{new}}=P^{-1}.[{\vec{\rm grad}}_{g}]_{|{old}}.

(S.22)

\bullet

df
cannot be identified
{\vec{\rm grad}}f
(with one?) (Recall; there is no natural canonical isomorphims between
E
and
E^{*}
.)
The differential
df
is also called the “covariant gradient”,
and any of its associated gradient vectors is also called the “contravariant gradient relative to an inner dot product”.

Isometric Euclidean framework:

If one Euclidean dot product can be imposed to all observers (foot? metre?) then
{\vec{\rm grad}}_{g}f{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{\rm grad}}f=\vec{\nabla}f
and (
S.19
) is written
df.{\vec{u}}={\vec{\rm grad}}f\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{\vec{u}}=\vec{\nabla}f\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{\vec{u}}
(isometric framework).

Exercice S.14

Cartesian basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
given by
[g]_{[{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{1&0\cr 0&2}
. Give

[df]_{|{\vec{e}}}
and
[{\vec{\rm grad}_{\!g}}f]_{|{\vec{e}}}
.

Answer
.
[df]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{{\partial f\over\partial x_{1}}&{\partial f\over\partial x_{2}}}
(row matrix)
and (
S.19
) gives
[{\vec{\rm grad}_{\!g}}f]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{{\partial f\over\partial x_{1}}\cr{1\over 2}{\partial f\over\partial x_{2}}}
(column matrix
\neq[df]^{T}
).

S.4
Application 2: Coordinate system basis and Christoffel symbols

(Necessary when dealing with covariance.)

S.4.1
Coordinate system, and coordinate system basis

Consider a (open) set
U_{\!\rm par}=\{{\vec{q}}\in]a_{1},b_{1}[\times...\times]a_{n},b_{n}[\}
, called the set of parameters,
in the Cartesian space
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
,
consider an open set
U\subset{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
, called the set of geometric positions,
and consider a
C^{2}
-diffeomorphism
\Psi:{\vec{q}}\in U_{\!\rm par}\rightarrow p\in U
, called a coordinate system.

Let
({\vec{a}}_{i})
the canonical basis of the parameter space, let
{\vec{q}}=\sum_{i}q_{i}{\vec{a}}_{i}\in U_{\!\rm par}
(the
q_{i}
are called the parameters).
E.g., see the polar coordinate system at §
6.6.2
where
{\vec{q}}=(q_{1},q_{2})=(r,\theta)
.

\Psi
being a diffeomorphism, at any
p=\Psi({\vec{q}})\in U
the vectors

{\vec{a}}_{i*}(p):=d\Psi({\vec{q}}).{\vec{a}}_{i}

(S.23)

make a basis in
E
at
p
, and
({\vec{a}}_{i*}(p))
is called the coordinate system basis at
p
.
Its dual basis at
p
is made of the linear forms
dq_{i}(p)
, so where, for all
i,j
,

dq_{i}(p).{\vec{a}}_{j*}(p)=\delta^{j}_{i}.

(S.24)

Duality notations:
dq^{i}(p).{\vec{a}}_{j*}(p)=\delta^{i}_{j}
for all
i,j
.

S.4.2
Parametric expression of the differential of a scalar valued function

With a coordinate system
\Psi
, a scalar valued function

f:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr U&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr p&\rightarrow f(p)\crcr}}\,\right\}

defined in
U
can be described with the function

g=f\circ\Psi:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr U_{\!\rm par}&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr{\vec{q}}&\rightarrow g({\vec{q}}):=f(p)\;\hbox{when}\;p=\Psi({\vec{q}})\crcr}}\,\right\}

defined in
U_{\!\rm par}
, and
g
is called the parametric expression of
f
. Thus

dg({\vec{q}})=df(p).d\Psi({\vec{q}})\quad\hbox{when}\quad p=\Psi({\vec{q}}),

(S.25)

in particular,

{\partial g\over\partial q_{j}}({\vec{q}}):=dg({\vec{q}}).{\vec{a}}_{j}=df(p).d\Psi({\vec{q}}).{\vec{a}}_{j}=df(p).{\vec{a}}_{j*}(p){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\partial f\over\partial q_{j}}(p).

(S.26)

Warning, pay attention:

f
is a function of
p
, not a function of
{\vec{q}}
, and the notations

{\partial f\over\partial q_{j}}(p)
means
:={\partial(f\circ\Psi)\over\partial q_{j}}({\vec{q}})
when
p=\Psi({\vec{q}})
, and nothing else.

Thus with
(dq_{j}(p))
the dual basis of the coordinate basis
({\vec{a}}_{i*}(p))
at
p
,

df(p)\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqpafcoor0})}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}{\partial f\over\partial q_{j}}(p)\,dq_{j}(p).

(S.27)

Duality notations:
df(p)=\sum_{j}{\partial f\over\partial q^{j}}(p)\,dq^{j}(p)
.

Remark S.15

Pay attention to the notations that could contradict themselves:

1- In
U_{\!\rm par}
the dual basis
({\pi_{ai}})
of the Cartesian basis
({\vec{a}}_{i})
is a uniform basis (independent of
{\vec{q}}
)…
and is (almost) never written
(dq_{i})
…

2- Indeed,
(dq_{i}(p))
is the name reserved for the dual basis of
({\vec{a}}_{i*}(p))
in the geometric space… Mind the notations!
E.g. for polar coordinates
(dq_{1}(p),dq_{2}(p))=(dr(p),d\theta(p))
is the dual basis of the polar coordinate system basis
({\vec{a}}_{1*}(p),{\vec{a}}_{2*}(p))
at
p
, cf. (
6.6.2
).

Exercice S.16

Bases
({\vec{a}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
at
p
.
A vector
{\vec{x}}
is expressed as
{\vec{x}}=\sum_{i}x_{a,i}{\vec{a}}_{i}=\sum_{i}x_{b,i}{\vec{b}}_{i}
.
Prove:

{\vec{b}}_{i}=\lambda{\vec{a}}_{i},\;\;\forall i\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;\framebox{$\displaystyle{\partial f\over\partial x_{b,i}}=\lambda{\partial f\over\partial x_{a,i}}$}\quad\hbox{or}\quad{\partial f\over\partial x_{a,i}}={\partial f\over\partial(\lambda x_{a,i})}.

(S.28)

(Change of unit formula.)
Duality notations:
{\partial f\over\partial x_{b}^{j}}=\lambda{\partial f\over\partial x_{a}^{j}}
.

Answer
.
df(p).{\vec{b}}_{j}(p)=\lambda df(p).{\vec{a}}_{j}(p)
(linearity of
df(p)
) reads (
S.28
).
(Or
[df]_{|{\vec{b}}}=[df]_{|{\vec{a}}}.P
with
P=\lambda I
here.)

Exercice S.17

[df]_{|{\vec{b}}}=[df]_{|{\vec{a}}}.P
, cf. (
S.17
), i.e.
{\partial f\over\partial x_{b}^{j}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\partial f\over\partial x_{a}^{i}}P^{i}_{j}
is also noted

{\partial f\over\partial x_{b}^{j}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\partial f\over\partial x_{a}^{i}}{\partial x_{a}^{i}\over\partial x_{b}^{j}}.

(S.29)

Why?

Answer
.
Quick answer.

[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}=P^{-1}.[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}
,
i.e.
[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}=P.[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}
,
which means
[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}([{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{b}}})=P.[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}
,
i.e.

\pmatrix{x_{a}^{1}(x_{b}^{1},...,x_{b}^{n})\cr\vdots\cr x_{a}^{1}(x_{b}^{1},...,x_{b}^{n})}=\pmatrix{{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}P^{1}_{j}x_{b}^{j}\cr\vdots\cr{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}P^{n}_{j}x_{b}^{j}},\hskip 9.24994pt\hbox{thus}\hskip 9.24994pt{\partial x_{a}^{i}\over\partial x_{b}^{j}}(x_{b}^{1},...,x_{b}^{n})=P^{i}_{j},\;\;\forall i,j.

(S.30)

Thus (
S.29
) means

{\partial f\over\partial x_{b}^{j}}(p)={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\partial f\over\partial x_{a}^{i}}(p){\partial x_{a}^{i}\over\partial x_{b}^{j}}(x_{b}^{1},...,x_{b}^{n})\hskip 9.24994pt\hbox{thus}\hskip 9.24994pt={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\partial f\over\partial x_{a}^{i}}(p)\,P^{i}_{j},

(S.31)

as given in (
S.17
).

Detailed answer.

Let
O
be a point (origin) in
U
. If
p\in U
,
let
{\vec{x}}=\overrightarrow{Op}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x_{a}^{i}{\vec{a}}_{i}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x_{b}^{i}{\vec{b}}_{i}
.

This define the function
[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}:[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}\rightarrow[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}([{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{b}}})
,
and we have
[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}([{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{b}}})=P.[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}
(change of basis formula).

Then let
f_{a},f_{b}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be defined by

f_{a}([{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}):=f(p)
and
f_{b}([{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}):=f(p)
.
(NB:
f_{a}
and
f_{b}
don’t have the same definition domain: They are different).

Thus
f_{a}([{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{a}}})=f_{b}([{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{b}}})
(
=f(p)
) when
[{\vec{x}}([{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{b}}})]_{|{\vec{a}}}=P.[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}
, so

(f_{a}\circ[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{a}}})([{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{b}}})=f_{b}([{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{b}}})
.
Thus
{\partial(f_{a}\circ[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{a}}})\over\partial x_{b}^{i}}([{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{b}}})={\partial f_{b}\over\partial x_{b}^{i}}([{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{b}}})
,
thus the meaning of (
S.29
) is

{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}{\partial f_{a}\over\partial x_{a}^{j}}([{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}){\partial x_{a}^{j}\over\partial x_{b}^{i}}([{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{b}}})={\partial f_{b}\over\partial x_{b}^{i}}([{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}).

(S.32)

Question:
Why did we introduce
f_{a}
and
f_{b}
(and not just keep
f
)?

Answer:
Because a vector is not just a collection of components (is not just a matrix),
and
\overrightarrow{Op}
cannot be reduced to a matrix of components (which one:
[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}
?
[{\vec{x}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}
?).
Here
f
is a function acting on a point
p
(independent of a referential), while
f_{a}
and
f_{b}
are functions acting on matrices (dependent on the choice of a referential):
The domain of definitions are different, so the functions
f
,
f_{a}
and
f_{b}
are different.

S.4.3
Christoffel symbols

We use duality notations for readability and usage.

Definition S.18

In a coordinate system basis
({\vec{e}}_{i}(p))
in
E
(previously called
({\vec{a}}_{i*}(p)
),
the Christoffel symbol
\gamma^{i}_{jk}(p)\in\mathbb{R}
are the components of the vector
d{\vec{e}}_{k}(p).{\vec{e}}_{j}(p)
,
i.e.
d{\vec{e}}_{k}(p).{\vec{e}}_{j}(p)={\sum_{k=1}^{n}}\gamma_{jk}^{i}(p){\vec{e}}_{i}(p)
, so

\framebox{$\displaystyle d{\vec{e}}_{k}.{\vec{e}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\gamma^{i}_{jk}{\vec{e}}_{i}$},\quad\hbox{or}\quad d{\vec{e}}_{j}.{\vec{e}}_{i}={\sum_{k=1}^{n}}\gamma^{k}_{ij}{\vec{e}}_{k}.

(S.33)

(So, with
(e^{i}(p))
the dual basis of
({\vec{e}}_{i}(p))
,
\gamma^{i}_{jk}:=e^{i}.d{\vec{e}}_{k}.{\vec{e}}_{j}
, and, for calculations with contractions,
d{\vec{e}}_{k}=\sum_{ij}\gamma^{i}_{jk}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
.)

(The Christoffel symbols vanish in a Cartesian framework.)

(Differential geometry in manifolds:
\nabla_{{\vec{e}}_{j}}{\vec{e}}_{k}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\gamma^{i}_{jk}{\vec{e}}_{i}
, i.e. the
\gamma^{i}_{jk}=e^{i}.\nabla_{{\vec{e}}_{j}}{\vec{e}}_{k}
are the component of the connection
\nabla
, the usual connection in a surface in
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
being the Riemannian connection, in which case
\nabla_{{\vec{e}}_{j}}{\vec{e}}_{k}
is the orthogonal projection of
d{\vec{e}}_{k}.{\vec{e}}_{j}
on the surface relative to a Euclidean dot product.)

E.g. for the polar coordinate system, see remark
6.12
,
d{\vec{e}}_{2}.{\vec{e}}_{2}=-r{\vec{e}}_{1}
, thus
\gamma^{1}_{22}=-r

and
\gamma^{2}_{22}=0
.

Exercice S.19

Prove: If
({\vec{e}}_{i}(p))
is the coordinate system basis of a
C^{2}
coordinate system, then:

\forall i,j,\;d{\vec{e}}_{i}.{\vec{e}}_{j}=d{\vec{e}}_{j}.{\vec{e}}_{i}\quad(={\partial^{2}\Psi\over\partial q^{i}\partial q^{j}}),\quad\hbox{and}\quad\forall i,j,k,\;\gamma^{k}_{ji}=\gamma^{k}_{ij}\quad\hbox{(symmetry for lower indices)}.

(S.34)

Answer
.
{\vec{e}}_{i}(p)=({\vec{e}}_{i}\circ\Psi)({\vec{q}})\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqveiPsi})}}d\Psi({\vec{q}}).{\vec{a}}_{i}
gives

d({\vec{e}}_{i}\circ\Psi)({\vec{q}}).{\vec{a}}_{j}=d(d\Psi({\vec{q}}).{\vec{a}}_{i}).{\vec{a}}_{j}
,
thus

d{\vec{e}}_{i}(\Psi({\vec{q}})).d\Psi({\vec{q}}).{\vec{a}}_{j}={\partial{\partial\Psi\over\partial q^{i}}\over\partial q^{j}}={\partial{\partial\Psi\over\partial q^{j}}\over\partial q^{i}}
(Schwarz theorem since
\Psi
is
C^{2}
)

=d{\vec{e}}_{j}.{\vec{e}}_{i}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\partial^{2}\Psi\over\partial q^{j}\partial q^{i}}
,
thus
{\sum_{k=1}^{n}}\gamma^{k}_{ij}{\vec{e}}_{k}={\sum_{k=1}^{n}}\gamma^{k}_{ji}{\vec{e}}_{k}
.

Exercice S.20

Consider two coordinate system bases
({\vec{a}}_{i}(p))
and
({\vec{b}}_{i}(p))
at
p
,

P(p)=[P^{i}_{j}(p)]
the transition matrix from
({\vec{a}}_{i}(p))
to
({\vec{b}}_{i}(p))
, and
Q=P^{-1}
.
Using the generic notation
d{\vec{e}}_{k}.{\vec{e}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\gamma^{i}_{jk,e}{\vec{e}}_{i}
, prove the change of basis formula for the Christoffel symbols:

\gamma_{jk,b}^{i}=\sum_{\lambda,\mu,\nu=1}^{n}Q^{i}_{\lambda}P^{\mu}_{j}P^{\nu}_{k}\gamma_{\mu\nu,a}^{\lambda}+\sum_{\lambda,\mu=1}^{n}Q^{i}_{\lambda}P^{\mu}_{j}(dP^{\lambda}_{k}.{\vec{a}}_{\mu})\quad(=\sum_{\lambda,\mu,\nu=1}^{n}Q^{i}_{\lambda}P^{\mu}_{j}P^{\nu}_{k}\gamma_{\mu\nu,a}^{\lambda}+\sum_{\lambda=1}^{n}Q^{i}_{\lambda}(dP^{\lambda}_{k}.{\vec{b}}_{j})).

(S.35)

(Because of the term
\sum_{\mu\nu}Q^{i}_{\lambda}P^{\mu}_{j}(dP^{\lambda}_{k}.{\vec{a}}_{\mu})
, a derivation is not a tensor.)

Answer
.
{\vec{b}}_{k}(p)=\sum_{\nu}P^{\nu}_{k}(p){\vec{a}}_{\nu}(p)
gives

d{\vec{b}}_{k}.{\vec{b}}_{j}=\sum_{\nu}(dP^{\nu}_{k}.{\vec{b}}_{j}){\vec{a}}_{\nu}+\sum_{\nu}P^{\nu}_{k}(d{\vec{a}}_{\nu}.{\vec{b}}_{j})=\sum_{\mu\nu}P^{\mu}_{j}(dP^{\nu}_{k}.{\vec{a}}_{\mu}){\vec{a}}_{\nu}+\sum_{\mu\nu}P^{\nu}_{k}P^{\mu}_{j}(d{\vec{a}}_{\nu}.{\vec{a}}_{\mu})
;
And
b^{i}=\sum_{\lambda}Q^{i}_{\lambda}a^{\lambda}
, thus

\gamma^{i}_{jk,b}=b^{i}.d{\vec{b}}_{k}.{\vec{b}}_{j}=\sum_{\lambda\mu\nu}Q^{i}_{\lambda}P^{\mu}_{j}(dP^{\nu}_{k}.{\vec{a}}_{\mu})a^{\lambda}.{\vec{a}}_{\nu}+\sum_{\lambda\mu\nu}Q^{i}_{\lambda}P^{\mu}_{j}P^{\nu}_{k}a^{\lambda}.(d{\vec{a}}_{\nu}.{\vec{a}}_{\mu})=\sum_{\lambda\mu}Q^{i}_{\lambda}P^{\mu}_{j}(dP^{\lambda}_{k}.{\vec{a}}_{\mu})+\sum_{\lambda\mu\nu}Q^{i}_{\lambda}P^{\mu}_{j}P^{\nu}_{k}\gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu,a},

thus (
S.35
).

S.5
Application 3: Differential of a vector field

Here
F=E={\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
,

\Phi{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{w}}\in\Gamma(U)
is a vector field.
Thus
d{\vec{w}}(p)\in{\cal L}(E;E)
and
d{\vec{w}}.{\vec{u}}
is a vector field in
E
for all
{\vec{u}}\in\Gamma(U)
, given by

(d{\vec{w}}.{\vec{u}})(p)=d{\vec{w}}(p).{\vec{u}}(p)=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{{\vec{w}}(p+h{\vec{u}}(p))-{\vec{w}}(p)\over h}\in E
.

Quantification:

({\vec{e}}_{i}(p))
is a basis at
p
in
E
. Call
w_{i}(p)\in\mathbb{R}
the components of
{\vec{w}}(p)
, i.e.
{\vec{w}}(p)={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}w_{i}(p){\vec{e}}_{i}(p)
.
And call
w_{i|j}(p)
the components of
d{\vec{w}}(p)
(endomorphism in
E
):

{\vec{w}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}w_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i},\quad d{\vec{w}}.{\vec{e}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}w_{i|j}{\vec{e}}_{i},\quad[d{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[w_{i|j}]\quad\hbox{(Jacobian matrix)}.

(S.36)

And tensorial notations for calculations with contractions:
(\pi_{ei}(p))
being the dual basis,

d{\vec{w}}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}w_{i|j}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes\pi_{ej}.

(S.37)

Duality notations:
{\vec{w}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}w^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,

d{\vec{w}}.{\vec{e}}_{j}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}w^{i}_{|j}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,
[d{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[w^{i}_{|j}]
,
and
d{\vec{w}}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}w^{i}_{|j}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
.

In a Cartesian basis:

Here
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is uniform,
so
{\vec{w}}(p)={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}w_{i}(p){\vec{e}}_{i}
gives
d{\vec{w}}(p).{\vec{e}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}(dw_{i}(p).{\vec{e}}_{j}){\vec{e}}_{i}
,
thus (
S.36
) gives

w_{i|j}={\partial w_{i}\over\partial x_{j}}(p){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}w_{i,j},\quad\hbox{so}\quad[d{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[{\partial w_{i}\over\partial x_{j}}].

(S.38)

Duality notations:
w^{i}_{|j}={\partial w^{i}\over\partial x^{j}}

and
[d{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[{\partial w^{i}\over\partial x^{j}}]
.

In a coordinate system basis:

With the coordinate system described in §
S.4
and the duality notations for readability (and usage).

{\vec{w}}(p)={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}w^{i}(p){\vec{e}}_{i}(p)
gives, for all
j
,

d{\vec{w}}.{\vec{e}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}(dw^{i}.{\vec{e}}_{j}){\vec{e}}_{i}+{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}w^{i}(d{\vec{e}}_{i}.{\vec{e}}_{j})\quad(={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}w^{i}_{|j}{\vec{e}}_{i}).

(S.39)

(Tensorial notations to be used with contractions:

d{\vec{w}}=\sum_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes dw^{i}+\sum_{i}w^{i}\,d{\vec{e}}_{i}=\sum_{ij}w^{i}_{|j}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
.)

And
\sum_{i}w^{i}(d{\vec{e}}_{i}.{\vec{e}}_{j})\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdefCs})}}\sum_{ik}w^{i}\gamma^{k}_{ji}{\vec{e}}_{k}=\sum_{ik}w^{k}\gamma^{i}_{jk}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,
thus, for all
i,j
,

\framebox{$\displaystyle w^{i}_{|j}={\partial w^{i}\over\partial q^{j}}+{\sum_{k=1}^{n}}w^{k}\gamma^{i}_{jk}$}\quad\hbox{where}\quad{\partial w^{i}\over\partial q^{j}}:=dw^{i}.{\vec{e}}_{j}.

(S.40)

(
{\partial w^{i}\over\partial q^{j}}:=dw^{i}.{\vec{e}}_{j}
is the derivation along the
j
-th coordinate line of the scalar valued function
w^{i}
).

(In particular, if
{\vec{w}}={\vec{e}}_{\ell}=\sum_{i}\delta^{i}_{\ell}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,
we recover
d{\vec{e}}_{\ell}.{\vec{e}}_{j}=\sum_{i}0{\vec{e}}_{i}+\sum_{ik}\delta^{k}_{\ell}\gamma^{i}_{jk}{\vec{e}}_{i}=\sum_{i}\gamma^{i}_{j\ell}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,
cf. (
S.33
).)

Exercice S.21

With exercise
S.20
, and
{\vec{w}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}u^{i}{\vec{a}}_{i}=\sum_{n}v^{i}{\vec{b}}_{i}
,
check with calculations (
d{\vec{w}}
is an endomorphism defined independently of any basis):

[d{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}=P^{-1}.[d{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}.P,\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad v^{i}_{|j}={\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{n}}Q^{i}_{k}u^{k}_{|\ell}P^{\ell}_{j}.

(S.41)

Answer
.
{\vec{b}}_{j}=\sum_{\ell}P^{\ell}_{j}{\vec{a}}_{\ell}
for all
i
,
Q=P^{|1}
, and
[{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}=Q.[{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}
reads

v^{i}=\sum_{k}Q^{i}_{k}u^{k}
for all
i
.

Cartesian basis:

dv^{i}.{\vec{b}}_{j}=d(\sum_{k}Q^{i}_{k}u^{k}).(\sum_{\ell}P^{\ell}_{j}{\vec{a}}_{\ell})=\sum_{k\ell}Q^{i}_{k}(du^{k}.{\vec{a}}_{\ell})P^{\ell}_{j}
, qed (here the
Q^{i}_{k}
are uniform i.e. independent of
p
).

Coordinate system basis:

v^{i}=\sum_{\lambda}Q^{i}_{\lambda}u^{\lambda}

gives
dv^{i}.{\vec{b}}_{j}=\sum_{\lambda}(dQ^{i}_{\lambda}.{\vec{b}}_{j})u^{\lambda}+\sum_{\lambda}Q^{i}_{\lambda}(du^{\lambda}.{\vec{b}}_{j})
; Thus

\displaystyle{v^{i}_{|j}\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdefCs})}}}

\displaystyle{{}dv^{i}.{\vec{b}}_{j}+\sum_{k}v^{k}\gamma^{i}_{jk,b}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}\sum_{\lambda\mu}u^{\lambda}P^{\mu}_{j}(dQ^{i}_{\lambda}.{\vec{a}}_{\mu})+\sum_{\lambda\mu}Q^{i}_{\lambda}P^{\mu}_{j}(du^{\lambda}.{\vec{a}}_{\mu})+\sum_{k\lambda\mu\nu}(Q^{k}_{\lambda}u^{\lambda})Q^{i}_{\nu}P^{\mu}_{j}(dP^{\nu}_{k}.{\vec{a}}_{\mu})+\sum_{k\omega\lambda\mu\nu}(Q^{k}_{\omega}u^{\omega})Q^{i}_{\lambda}P^{\mu}_{j}P^{\nu}_{k}\gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu,a}}

And
Q^{k}_{\omega}P^{\lambda}_{k}=\delta^{\lambda}_{\omega}
gives
(dQ^{k}_{\omega}.{\vec{a}}_{\mu})P^{\lambda}_{k}+Q^{k}_{\omega}(dP^{\lambda}_{k}.{\vec{a}}_{\mu})=0
,
thus the third term reads

\sum_{k\lambda\mu\nu}u^{\lambda}Q^{i}_{\nu}P^{\mu}_{j}Q^{k}_{\lambda}(dP^{\nu}_{k}.{\vec{a}}_{\mu})=-\sum_{k\lambda\mu\nu}u^{\lambda}Q^{i}_{\nu}P^{\mu}_{j}P^{\nu}_{k}(dQ^{k}_{\lambda}.{\vec{a}}_{\mu})=-\sum_{\lambda\mu}u^{\lambda}P^{\mu}_{j}(dQ^{i}_{\lambda}.{\vec{a}}_{\mu}),

which cancels the first term:
Thus

v^{i}_{|j}=\sum_{\lambda\mu}Q^{i}_{\lambda}P^{\mu}_{j}(du^{\lambda}.{\vec{a}}_{\mu})+\sum_{\lambda\mu\nu}u^{\nu}Q^{i}_{\lambda}P^{\mu}_{j}\gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}=\sum_{\lambda\mu}Q^{i}_{\lambda}u^{i}_{|j}P^{\mu}_{j}
, i.e. (
S.41
).

S.6
Application 4: Differential of a differential form

Here
F=\mathbb{R}
,
\Phi{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\ell\in\Omega^{1}(U)
(differential form) supposed
C^{1}
,
p\in U
, so
\ell(p)\in E^{*}
.
Its differential at
p
in a direction
{\vec{u}}
is

d\ell(p).{\vec{u}}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{\ell(p+h{\vec{u}})-\ell(p)\over h}\;\in{E^{*}}.

And
(d\ell(p).{\vec{u}}).{\vec{v}}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{\ell(p+h{\vec{u}}).{\vec{v}}-\ell(p).{\vec{v}}\over h}\;\in\mathbb{R}

for all
{\vec{u}},{\vec{v}}\in E
.

Quantification:

(\pi_{ei}(p)
its the dual basis.

Call
\ell_{i}(p)\in\mathbb{R}
the components of
\ell(p)
, i.e.
\ell(p)={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\ell_{i}(p)\pi_{ei}(p)
.
And call
\ell_{i|j}(p)
the components of
d\ell(p)\in{\cal L}(E;E^{*})
:

\ell={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\ell_{i}\pi_{ei},\quad d\ell.{\vec{e}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\ell_{i|j}\pi_{ei},\quad[d\ell]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[\ell_{i|j}].

(S.42)

Tensorial notations, to be used with contractions:

d\ell={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}\ell_{i|j}\pi_{ei}\otimes\pi_{ej}.

Duality notations:
\ell=\sum_{i}\ell_{i}e^{i}
,
d\ell.{\vec{e}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\ell_{i|j}e^{i}
,
[d\ell]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[\ell_{i|j}]
, and

d\ell={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}\ell_{i|j}e^{i}\otimes e^{j}
.

In a Cartesian basis:

Here
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is uniform, so

\ell_{i|j}={\partial\ell_{i}\over\partial x_{j}}(p){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\ell_{i,j},\quad\hbox{so}\quad[d\ell]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[{\partial\ell_{i}\over\partial x_{j}}].

(S.43)

Duality notations:
\ell_{i|j}=d\ell_{i}.{\vec{e}}_{j}={\partial\ell_{i}\over\partial x^{j}}
and
[d\ell]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[{\partial\ell_{i}\over\partial x^{j}}]
.

In a coordinate system basis:
With duality notations and Christoffel symbols:

\framebox{$\displaystyle de^{i}.{\vec{e}}_{j}=-{\sum_{k=1}^{n}}\gamma^{i}_{jk}e^{k}$}.

(S.44)

Indeed,

e^{i}.{\vec{e}}_{k}=\delta^{i}_{k}
gives
(de^{i}.{\vec{e}}_{j}).{\vec{e}}_{k}+e^{i}.(d{\vec{e}}_{k}.{\vec{e}}_{j})=0
,
thus
(de^{i}.{\vec{e}}_{j}).{\vec{e}}_{k}=-e^{i}.\sum_{\ell}\gamma^{\ell}_{jk}{\vec{e}}_{\ell}=-\gamma^{i}_{jk}
. Thus

\framebox{$\displaystyle\ell_{i|j}={\partial\ell_{i}\over\partial q^{j}}-{\sum_{k=1}^{n}}\ell_{k}\gamma^{k}_{ji}$}\quad\hbox{where}\quad{\partial\ell_{i}\over\partial q^{j}}(p):=d\ell_{i}(p).{\vec{e}}_{i}(p).

(S.45)

Indeed,

\ell=\sum_{i}\ell_{i}e^{i}
gives

d\ell.{\vec{e}}_{j}=\sum_{i}(d\ell_{i}.{\vec{e}}_{j})e^{i}+\sum_{i}\ell_{i}(de^{i}.{\vec{e}}_{j})=\sum_{i}(d\ell_{i}.{\vec{e}}_{j})e^{i}-\sum_{ik}\ell_{i}\gamma^{i}_{jk}e^{k}
.

S.7
Application 5: Differential of a 1 1 tensor

Consider a
C^{1}

{1\choose 1}
tensor
{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr U&\rightarrow{\cal L}({E^{*}},E;\mathbb{R})\cr p&\rightarrow{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}(p)\cr}}\,\right\}
. Its differential

d{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr U&\rightarrow{\cal L}(E;{\cal L}({E^{*}},E;\mathbb{R}))\cr p&\rightarrow d{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}(p)\cr}}\,\right\}

is defined by
d{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}(p).{\vec{u}}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}(p+h{\vec{u}})-{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}(p)\over h}\in{\cal L}({E^{*}},E;\mathbb{R})
,
so
(d{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}(p).{\vec{u}})(\ell,{\vec{v}})=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}(p+h{\vec{u}})(\ell,{\vec{v}})-{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}(p)(\ell,{\vec{v}})\over h}

(
\in\mathbb{R}
), for all
{\vec{u}},{\vec{v}}\in E
and
\ell\in E^{*}
.

Quantification
(duality notations):
Basis
({\vec{e}}_{i}(p))
in
E
at
p
, dual basis
(e^{i}(p))
,
call
\tau^{i}_{j}(p)
the components of
{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}(p)
,
call
\tau^{i}_{j|k}(p)
the components of
d{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}(p)
:

{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}=\sum_{ij}\tau_{ij}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j},\quad\framebox{$\displaystyle d{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}.{\vec{e}}_{k}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}\tau^{i}_{j|k}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}$}.

(S.46)

Tensorial notations, to be used with contractions:

d{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}={\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}}\tau^{i}_{j|k}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}\otimes e^{k}.

(Classical notations:
{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}=\sum_{ij}\tau_{ij}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes\pi_{ej}
,

d{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}.{\vec{e}}_{k}=\sum_{ij}\tau_{ij|k}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes\pi_{ej}
, and

d{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}=\sum_{ijk}\tau_{ij|k}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes\pi_{ej}\otimes\pi_{ek}
.)

Cartesian basis:

d{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}(p).{\vec{e}}_{k}=\sum_{ij}(d\tau^{i}_{j}(p).{\vec{e}}_{k}){\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
,
so

\tau^{i}_{j|k}={\partial\tau^{i}_{j}\over\partial x^{k}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\tau^{i}_{j,k}\quad(:=d\tau^{i}_{j}.{\vec{e}}_{k}).

(S.47)

Coordinate system basis:

{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}(p)={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}\tau^{i}_{j}(p){\vec{e}}_{i}(p)\otimes e^{j}(p)
gives, for all
k
,

\displaystyle{d{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}.{\vec{e}}_{k}=}

\displaystyle{{}\textstyle\sum_{ij}(d\tau^{i}_{j}.{\vec{e}}_{k}){\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}+\sum_{ij}\tau^{i}_{j}(d{\vec{e}}_{i}.{\vec{e}}_{k})\otimes e^{j}+\sum_{ij}\tau^{i}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes(de^{j}.{\vec{e}}_{k})}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}\textstyle\sum_{ij}(d\tau^{i}_{j}.{\vec{e}}_{k}){\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}+\sum_{ij\ell}\tau^{i}_{j}\gamma^{\ell}_{ki}{\vec{e}}_{\ell}\otimes e^{j}-\sum_{ij\ell}\tau^{i}_{j}\gamma^{j}_{k\ell}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{\ell}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}\textstyle\sum_{ij}(d\tau^{i}_{j}.{\vec{e}}_{k}){\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}+\sum_{ij\ell}\tau^{\ell}_{j}\gamma^{i}_{k\ell}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}-\sum_{ij\ell}\tau^{i}_{\ell}\gamma^{\ell}_{kj}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}}

(S.48)

thus

\framebox{$\displaystyle\tau^{i}_{j|k}={\partial\tau^{i}_{j}\over\partial q^{k}}+{\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}}\tau^{\ell}_{j}\gamma^{i}_{k\ell}-{\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}}\tau^{i}_{\ell}\gamma^{\ell}_{kj}$}\quad\hbox{where}\quad{\partial\tau^{i}_{j}\over\partial q^{k}}:=d\tau^{i}_{j}.{\vec{e}}_{k}.

(S.49)

(We have the
+
sign from vector fields, cf. (
S.40
), and the
-
sign from differential forms, cf. (
S.45
).)

Exercice S.22

If
{\vec{u}}\in E
,
\ell\in E^{*}
then for the elementary
{1\choose 1}
tensor
{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}={\vec{u}}\otimes\ell
prove:

d({\vec{u}}\otimes\ell).{\vec{e}}_{k}=(d{\vec{u}}.{\vec{e}}_{k})\otimes\ell+{\vec{u}}\otimes(d\ell.{\vec{e}}_{k}),\quad\hbox{and}\quad({\vec{u}}\otimes\ell)^{i}_{j|k}=u^{i}_{|k}\ell_{j}+u^{i}\ell_{j|k},

(S.50)

when
{\vec{u}}=\sum_{i}u^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,

\ell=\sum_{j}\ell_{j}e^{j}
,

d{\vec{u}}.{\vec{e}}_{k}=\sum_{i}u^{i}_{|k}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,

d\ell.{\vec{e}}_{k}=\sum_{j}\ell_{j|k}e^{j}
.

Answer
.
{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}={\vec{u}}\otimes\ell=\sum_{ij}\tau^{i}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
.
where
\tau^{i}_{j}=u^{i}\ell_{j}
, and

d{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}.{\vec{e}}_{k}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}\tau^{i}_{j|k}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
where

\tau^{i}_{j|k}=(u^{i}\ell_{j})_{|k}=u^{i}_{|k}\ell_{j}+u^{i}\ell_{j|k}=({\vec{u}}\otimes\ell)^{i}_{j|k}
. Thus
(similar to the derivation of a product):

\displaystyle{d({\vec{u}}\otimes\ell)(p).{\vec{e}}_{k}(p)=}

\displaystyle{{}\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{({\vec{u}}\otimes\ell)(p{+}h{\vec{e}}_{k}(p))-({\vec{u}}\otimes\ell)(p)\over h}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{{\vec{u}}(p{+}h{\vec{e}}_{k}(p))\otimes\ell(p{+}h{\vec{e}}_{k}(p))-{\vec{u}}(p)\otimes\ell(p)\over h}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{{\vec{u}}(p{+}h{\vec{e}}_{k}(p))\otimes\ell(p{+}h{\vec{e}}_{k}(p))-{\vec{u}}(p{+}h{\vec{e}}_{k}(p))\otimes\ell(p)\over h}+\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}{{\vec{u}}(p{+}h{\vec{e}}_{k}(p))\otimes\ell(p)-{\vec{u}}(p)\otimes\ell(p)\over h}}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}({\vec{u}}(p{+}h{\vec{e}}_{k}(p))\otimes({\ell(p{+}h{\vec{e}}_{k}(p))-\ell(p)\over h})+\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}({{\vec{u}}(p{+}h{\vec{e}}_{k}(p))-{\vec{u}}(p)\over h})\otimes\ell(p)}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}{\vec{u}}(p)\otimes(d\ell(p).{\vec{e}}_{k}(p))+(d{\vec{u}}(p).{\vec{e}}_{k}(p))\otimes\ell(p),}

thus (
S.50
)
1
.
Which gives
d({\vec{u}}\otimes\ell).{\vec{e}}_{k}=(\sum_{i}u^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i})\otimes(\sum_{j}\ell_{j|k}e^{j})+(\sum_{i}u^{i}_{|k}{\vec{e}}_{i})\otimes(\sum_{j}\ell_{j}e^{j})
, thus (
S.50
)
2
.

S.8
Divergence of a vector field: Invariant

\Gamma(U)
is the set of
C^{1}
vector fields in
U
, and
{\rm Tr}:{\cal L}(E;E)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the trace operator.

Definition S.23

The divergence operator is

{\rm div}:={\rm Tr}\circ d:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr\Gamma(U)&\rightarrow C^{0}(U;\mathbb{R})\cr{\vec{w}}&\rightarrow{\rm div}{\vec{w}}:={\rm Tr}(d{\vec{w}}),\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad{\rm div}{\vec{w}}(p):={\rm Tr}(d{\vec{w}}(p)).\crcr}}\,\right.

(S.51)

(So
{\rm div}{\vec{w}}(p)=
trace of the endomorphism
d{\vec{w}}(p)
).

{\rm Tr}
and
d
are linear, hence
{\rm div}={\rm Tr}\circ d
is
\mathbb{R}
-linear (composed of two
\mathbb{R}
-linear maps).

Proposition S.24

The divergence of a vector field is objective (is an invariant): Same value for all observers (objective quantity) intrinsic to
{\vec{w}}
.

Proof.
The differential and the trace are objective.
(Or computation:
{\vec{w}}=\sum_{i}u^{i}{\vec{a}}_{i}=\sum_{i}v^{i}{\vec{b}}_{i}
gives
v^{i}_{|j}=\sum_{k\ell}Q^{i}_{k}u^{k}_{|\ell}P^{\ell}_{j}
, see (
S.41
), thus

\sum_{i}v^{i}_{|i}=\sum_{ik\ell}P^{\ell}_{i}Q^{i}_{k}u^{k}_{|\ell}=\sum_{k\ell}\delta^{\ell}_{k}u^{k}_{|\ell}=\sum_{k}u^{k}_{|k}
.)

Quantification:

{\vec{w}}\in\Gamma(U)
,
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is a basis,
{\vec{w}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}w_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
with classical notations, and
w_{i|j}(p)
are the components of the vector
d{\vec{w}}(p).{\vec{e}}_{j}(p)
in the basis
({\vec{e}}_{i}(p))
. Thus

\framebox{$\displaystyle{\rm div}{\vec{w}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}w_{i|i}$}.

(S.52)

Duality notations:

{\vec{w}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}w^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,
d{\vec{w}}.{\vec{e}}_{j}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}w^{i}_{|j}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,
[d{\vec{w}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=[w^{i}_{|j}]
,
{\rm div}{\vec{w}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}w^{i}_{|i}
.

Cartesian basis

({\vec{e}}_{i})
(classical notations):
dw_{i}.{\vec{e}}_{j}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\partial w_{i}\over\partial x_{j}}
and

w_{i|j}={\partial w_{i}\over\partial x_{i}},\quad\hbox{thus}\quad{\rm div}{\vec{w}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\partial w_{i}\over\partial x_{i}}.

(S.53)

Coordinate system basis

({\vec{e}}_{i})
(duality notations): With the Christoffel symbols, cf. (
S.33
), (
S.40
) gives

w^{i}_{|i}={\partial w^{i}\over\partial q^{i}}+{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}w^{k}\gamma^{i}_{ik},\quad\hbox{thus}\quad{\rm div}{\vec{w}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{\partial w^{i}\over\partial q^{i}}+{\sum_{i,k=1}^{n}}w^{k}\gamma^{i}_{ik}.

(S.54)

Exercice S.25

Prove:

{\rm div}(f{\vec{w}})=df.{\vec{w}}+f\,{\rm div}{\vec{w}}.

(S.55)

Answer
.
d(f{\vec{w}})={\vec{w}}\otimes df+f\,d{\vec{w}}
, thus
{\rm Tr}(d(f{\vec{w}}))={\rm Tr}({\vec{w}}\otimes df)+{\rm Tr}(f\,d{\vec{w}})=df.{\vec{w}}+f\,{\rm Tr}(d{\vec{w}})
. Use a coordinate system if you prefer.

Remark S.26

If
\alpha
is a differential form, if
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is a basis and
(e^{i})
its dual basis,
and if
\alpha={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\alpha_{i}e^{i}
, then
d\alpha={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\alpha_{i|j}e^{i}\otimes e^{j}
,
with
\alpha_{i|j}:={\vec{e}}_{i}.d\alpha.{\vec{e}}_{j}
.
Here it is impossible to define an objective trace of
d\alpha
like
{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\alpha_{i|i}
: The result depends on the choice of the basis (the Einstein convention is not satisfied, and e.g. with a Euclidean basis the result depends on the choice of unit of length: Foot? Meter?). Thus the objective (or intrinsic) divergence of a differential form is a nonsense.

S.9
Objective divergence for 1 1 tensors

To create an objective divergence for a second order
{1\choose 1}
tensor
{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}\in{T^{1}_{1}(U)}
, in (
S.46
) we have to contract an admissible index with the “differential index
k
”, So, no choice: Contract
i
and
k
to get
{\widetilde{\rm div}}{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}:={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}\tau^{i}_{j|i}e^{j}
.
Let us start with:

Definition S.27

Let
{\vec{u}}\in\Gamma(U)
and
\ell\in\Omega^{1}(U)
be
C^{1}
.
The objective divergence of the elementary
{1\choose 1}
tensor
{\vec{u}}\otimes\ell\in{T^{1}_{1}(U)}
is the differential form

{\widetilde{\rm div}}({\vec{u}}\otimes\ell)\in\Omega^{1}(U)
defined by

{\widetilde{\rm div}}({\vec{u}}\otimes\ell)=({\rm div}{\vec{u}})\ell+d\ell.{\vec{u}},

(S.56)

i.e. defined by
{\widetilde{\rm div}}({\vec{u}}\otimes\ell).{\vec{w}}=({\rm div}{\vec{u}})(\ell.{\vec{w}})+(d\ell.{\vec{w}}){\vec{u}}
for all
{\vec{w}}\in E
.
And the objective divergence operator

{\widetilde{\rm div}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{T^{1}_{1}(U)}&\rightarrow\Omega^{1}(U)\cr{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}&\rightarrow{\widetilde{\rm div}}{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}\cr}}\,\right\}
is the linear map defined on elementary tensors with (
S.56
).

Quantification:

({\vec{e}}_{i})
is a basis,
(e^{i})
its dual basis,

{\vec{u}}=\sum_{i}u^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
,

\ell=\sum_{j}\ell_{j}e^{j}
.
Thus
{\vec{u}}\otimes\ell=\sum_{ij}u^{i}\ell_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
,
and (
S.50
)-(
S.56
) give

{\widetilde{\rm div}}({\vec{u}}\otimes\ell)={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}(u^{i}_{|i}\ell_{j}+\ell_{j|i}u^{i})e^{j}.

(S.57)

So for an elementary tensor
{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}={\vec{u}}\otimes\ell
,
{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}=\sum_{ij}\tau^{i}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
,
and
d{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}.{\vec{e}}_{k}=\sum_{ijk}\tau^{i}_{j|k}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}

and
{\widetilde{\rm div}}({\underline{\underline{\tau}}})=\sum_{ij}\tau^{i}_{j|i}e^{i}
, with

\tau^{i}_{j|k}=u^{i}_{|k}\ell_{j}+u^{i}\ell_{j|k}
, here with
\tau^{i}_{j}=u^{i}\ell_{j}
,
and
\tau^{i}_{j|k}=u^{i}_{|k}\ell_{j}+u^{i}\ell_{j|k}
, so

\tau^{i}_{j|i}=u^{i}_{|i}\ell_{j}+u^{i}\ell_{j|i}
.

Thus, by linearity of
{\widetilde{\rm div}}
, for all tensors
{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}\in{T^{1}_{1}(U)}
, we have with (
S.46
):

\framebox{$\displaystyle{\widetilde{\rm div}}{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}\tau^{i}_{j|i}e^{j}$},\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad[{\widetilde{\rm div}}{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{\sum_{i}\tau^{i}_{1|i}&...\sum_{i}\tau^{i}_{n|i}}

(S.58)

(row matrix since
{\widetilde{\rm div}}{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}
is a differential form). I.e., we have contracted
i
and
k
in (
S.46
).

(Classical notations:
{\widetilde{\rm div}}{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}:={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}\tau_{ij|i}\pi_{ej}
, i.e.
[{\widetilde{\rm div}}{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}]_{|{\vec{e}}}=\pmatrix{\sum_{i}\tau_{i1|i}&...\sum_{i}\tau_{in|i}}
.)

So

\displaystyle{{}\hbox{Cartesian bases: }{\widetilde{\rm div}}{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}{\partial\tau^{i}_{j}\over\partial x^{i}}e^{j},}

\displaystyle{{}\hbox{Coord. sys. bases: }{\widetilde{\rm div}}{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}\bigl({\partial\tau^{i}_{j}\over\partial q^{i}}+{\sum_{k=1}^{n}}\tau^{k}_{j}\gamma^{i}_{ik}-{\sum_{k=1}^{n}}\tau^{k}_{i}\gamma^{i}_{kj}\bigr)e^{j}.}

(S.59)

Indeed: With
{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}=\sum_{j}(\sum_{i}\tau^{i}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i})\otimes e^{j}=\sum_{j}{\vec{w}}_{j}\otimes e^{j}

where
{\vec{w}}_{j}=\sum_{i}\tau^{i}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}
, the linearity of
{\widetilde{\rm div}}
gives

{\widetilde{\rm div}}{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}=\sum_{j}{\widetilde{\rm div}}({\vec{w}}_{j}\otimes e^{j})
; Thus, with (
S.56
):
1-
Cartesian basis:

{\rm div}{\vec{w}}_{j}=\sum_{i}{\partial\tau^{i}_{j}\over\partial x^{i}}

and
de^{j}=0
give

{\widetilde{\rm div}}{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}=\sum_{j}\sum_{i}{\partial\tau^{i}_{j}\over\partial x^{i}}e^{j}=\sum_{ij}\tau^{i}_{j,i}e^{j}
, thus (
S.59
)
1
; And
2-
Coordinate system basis:

{\rm div}{\vec{w}}_{j}\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqCh1d})}}\sum_{i}{\partial\tau^{i}_{j}\over\partial q^{i}}+\sum_{ik}\tau^{k}_{j}\gamma^{i}_{ik}

and
de^{j}.{\vec{w}}_{j}=\sum_{k}\tau^{k}_{j}de^{j}.{\vec{e}}_{k}=\sum_{k}\tau^{k}_{j}(-\sum_{i}\gamma^{j}_{ki}e^{i})
,
thus
\sum_{j}de^{j}.{\vec{w}}_{j}=-\sum_{ijk}\tau^{k}_{j}\gamma^{j}_{ki}e^{i}=-\sum_{ijk}\tau^{k}_{i}\gamma^{i}_{kj}e^{j}
, thus (
S.59
)
2
.

Exercice S.28

Prove: If
f\in C^{1}(U;\mathbb{R})
and
{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}\tau^{i}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}\in{T^{1}_{1}(U)}\cap C^{1}
then

{\widetilde{\rm div}}(f{\underline{\underline{\tau}}})=df.{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}+f\,{\widetilde{\rm div}}{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}.

(S.60)

Answer
.
f{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}=\sum_{ij}f\tau^{i}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
gives

d(f{\underline{\underline{\tau}}})=\sum_{ijk}(f\tau^{i}_{j})_{|k}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}\otimes e^{k}=\sum_{ijk}(f_{|k}\tau^{i}_{j}+f\tau^{i}_{j|k}){\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}\otimes e^{k}
, thus

{\widetilde{\rm div}}(f{\underline{\underline{\tau}}})=\sum_{ij}(f_{|i}\tau^{i}_{j}+f\tau^{i}_{j|i})e^{j}
;
And

df.{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}+f\,{\widetilde{\rm div}}{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}=\sum_{ij}f_{|i}\tau^{i}_{j}e^{j}+f\,\sum_{ij}\tau^{i}_{j|i}e^{j}
.

Exercice S.29

Prove: If
{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}\in{T^{1}_{1}(U)}
and
{\vec{w}}\in\Gamma(U)
then

\framebox{$\displaystyle{\rm div}({\underline{\underline{\tau}}}.{\vec{w}})={\widetilde{\rm div}}({\underline{\underline{\tau}}}).{\vec{w}}+{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}\mathop{\;\raise-1.55pt\hbox{\large$0$}\mkern-9.5mu\mbox{\kern 1.0pt\vbox to4.30554pt{\hbox{.}\vss\vss\hbox{.}}}\;}d{\vec{w}}$}.

(S.61)

Answer
.
{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}=\sum_{ij}\tau^{i}_{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes e^{j}
and
{\vec{w}}=\sum_{i}w^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
give

{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}.{\vec{w}}=\sum_{ij}\tau^{i}_{j}w^{j}{\vec{e}}_{i}
, thus

{\rm div}({\underline{\underline{\tau}}}.{\vec{w}})=\sum_{ij}\tau^{i}_{j|i}w^{j}+\tau^{i}_{j}w^{j}_{|i}
.

Exercice S.30

If
{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}\in{T^{1}_{1}(U)}
check with component calculations (since
{\widetilde{\rm div}}({\underline{\underline{\tau}}})
is objective):

[{\widetilde{\rm div}}({\underline{\underline{\tau}}})]_{|b}=[{\widetilde{\rm div}}({\underline{\underline{\tau}}})]_{|a}.P\quad(\hbox{covariance formula}),

(S.62)

where
P
is the transition matrix from a basis
({\vec{a}}_{i})
to a basis
({\vec{b}}_{i})
.

Answer
. Let
{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}=\sum_{ij}\sigma^{i}_{j}{\vec{a}}_{i}\otimes a^{j}=\sum_{ij}\tau^{i}_{j}{\vec{b}}_{i}\otimes b^{j}
,
so
\tau^{i}_{j}=\sum_{\lambda\mu}Q^{i}_{\lambda}\sigma^{\lambda}_{\mu}P^{\mu}_{j}
.

1- Cartesian bases:
\sum_{i}\tau^{i}_{j|i}=\sum_{i}d\tau^{i}_{j}.{\vec{b}}_{i}=\sum_{i}d(\sum_{\lambda\mu}Q^{i}_{\lambda}\sigma^{\lambda}_{\mu}P^{\mu}_{j}).(\sum_{\nu}P^{\nu}_{i}.{\vec{a}}_{\nu})=\sum_{i\lambda\mu\nu}Q^{i}_{\lambda}P^{\mu}_{j}P^{\nu}_{i}(d\sigma^{\lambda}_{\mu}.{\vec{a}}_{\nu})=\sum_{\lambda\mu\nu}\delta^{\nu}_{\lambda}P^{\mu}_{j}(d\sigma^{\lambda}_{\mu}.{\vec{a}}_{\nu})=\sum_{\lambda\mu}P^{\mu}_{j}(d\sigma^{\lambda}_{\mu}.{\vec{a}}_{\lambda})=\sum_{\mu}(\sum_{\lambda}\sigma^{\lambda}_{\mu|\lambda})P^{\mu}_{j}
as desired.

2- Coordinate system bases:

\sum_{i}\tau^{i}_{j|i}\mathbin{\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdvguutaucoor})}}\sum_{i}d\tau^{i}_{j}.{\vec{e}}_{i}+\sum_{i\ell}\tau^{\ell}_{j}\gamma^{i}_{i\ell,b}-\sum_{i\ell}\tau^{i}_{\ell}\gamma^{\ell}_{ij,b}

(with
j
fixed); With

\displaystyle{\sum_{i}(d\tau^{i}_{j}.{\vec{b}}_{i})=}

\displaystyle{{}\sum_{i\lambda\mu}Q^{i}_{\lambda}\,(d\sigma^{\lambda}_{\mu}.{\vec{b}}_{i})\,P^{\mu}_{j}+\sum_{i\lambda\mu}(dQ^{i}_{\lambda}.{\vec{b}}_{i})\,\sigma^{\lambda}_{\mu}\,P^{\mu}_{j}+\sum_{i\lambda\mu}Q^{i}_{\lambda}\,\sigma^{\lambda}_{\mu}\,(dP^{\mu}_{j}.{\vec{b}}_{i})}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}\sum_{i\lambda\mu\nu}Q^{i}_{\lambda}P^{\mu}_{j}P^{\nu}_{i}(d\sigma^{\lambda}_{\mu}.{\vec{a}}_{\nu})+\sum_{i\lambda\mu\nu}\sigma^{\lambda}_{\mu}\,P^{\mu}_{j}P^{\nu}_{i}(dQ^{i}_{\lambda}.{\vec{a}}_{\nu})+\sum_{i\lambda\mu\nu}\sigma^{\lambda}_{\mu}\,Q^{i}_{\lambda}P^{\nu}_{i}(dP^{\mu}_{j}.{\vec{a}}_{\nu})}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}\sum_{\lambda\mu}P^{\mu}_{j}(d\sigma^{\lambda}_{\mu}.{\vec{a}}_{\lambda})-\sum_{i\lambda\mu\nu}\sigma^{\lambda}_{\mu}\,P^{\mu}_{j}Q^{i}_{\lambda}(dP^{\nu}_{i}.{\vec{a}}_{\nu})+\sum_{\lambda\mu}\sigma^{\lambda}_{\mu}\,(dP^{\mu}_{j}.{\vec{a}}_{\lambda})}

since
P^{\nu}_{i}Q^{i}_{\lambda}=\delta^{\nu}_{\lambda}
gives

P^{\nu}_{i}(dQ^{i}_{\lambda}.{\vec{a}}_{\nu})-Q^{i}_{\lambda}(dP^{\nu}_{i}.{\vec{a}}_{\nu})
.
And, with (
S.35
),

\displaystyle{\sum_{i\ell}\tau^{\ell}_{j}\gamma^{i}_{i\ell,b}=}

\displaystyle{{}\sum_{i\ell}(\sum_{\lambda\mu}Q^{\ell}_{\lambda}\sigma^{\lambda}_{\mu}P^{\mu}_{j})(\sum_{\alpha\beta\omega}Q^{i}_{\alpha}P^{\beta}_{i}P^{\omega}_{\ell}\gamma_{\beta\omega,a}^{\alpha}+\sum_{\alpha\beta}Q^{i}_{\alpha}P^{\beta}_{i}(dP^{\alpha}_{\ell}.{\vec{a}}_{\beta}))}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}\sum_{\lambda\mu\alpha}\sigma^{\lambda}_{\mu}P^{\mu}_{j}\gamma_{\alpha\lambda,a}^{\alpha}+\sum_{\ell\lambda\mu\alpha}\sigma^{\lambda}_{\mu}Q^{\ell}_{\lambda}P^{\mu}_{j}(dP^{\alpha}_{\ell}.{\vec{a}}_{\alpha}),}

(S.63)

and

\displaystyle{-\sum_{i\ell}\tau^{i}_{\ell}\gamma^{\ell}_{ij,b}=}

\displaystyle{{}-\sum_{i\ell}(\sum_{\lambda\mu}Q^{i}_{\lambda}\sigma^{\lambda}_{\mu}P^{\mu}_{\ell})(\sum_{\alpha\beta\omega}P^{\alpha}_{i}P^{\beta}_{j}Q^{\ell}_{\omega}\gamma_{\alpha\beta,a}^{\omega}+\sum_{\alpha\omega}P^{\alpha}_{i}Q^{\ell}_{\omega}(dP^{\omega}_{j}.{\vec{a}}_{\alpha}))}

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle{{}-\sum_{\lambda\mu\beta}\sigma^{\lambda}_{\mu}P^{\beta}_{j}\gamma_{\lambda\beta,a}^{\mu}-\sum_{\lambda\mu}\sigma^{\lambda}_{\mu}(dP^{\mu}_{j}.{\vec{a}}_{\lambda}).}

(S.64)

Thus

\sum_{i}\tau^{i}_{j|i}=\sum_{\lambda\mu}P^{\mu}_{j}(d\sigma^{\lambda}_{\mu}.{\vec{a}}_{\lambda})+\sum_{\lambda\mu\alpha}\sigma^{\lambda}_{\mu}P^{\mu}_{j}\gamma_{\alpha\lambda,a}^{\alpha}-\sum_{\lambda\mu\beta}\sigma^{\lambda}_{\mu}P^{\beta}_{j}\gamma_{\lambda\beta,a}^{\mu}=\sum_{\lambda\mu}P^{\mu}_{j}\sigma^{\lambda}_{\mu|\lambda}
as desired.

S.9.1
Divergence of a 2 0 tensor

Let
{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}\in{T^{2}_{0}(U)}
and
{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}\tau^{ij}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes{\vec{e}}_{j}
,
thus
d{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}={\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}}\tau^{ij}_{\;\;|k}{\vec{e}}_{i}\otimes{\vec{e}}_{j}\otimes e^{k}
;
Then two objective divergences may be defined: by contracting
k
with
i
,
or
k
with
j
. (The Einstein convention is then satisfied.)

S.9.2
Divergence of a 0 2 tensor

Let
{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}={\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}\tau_{ij}e^{i}\otimes e^{j}\in{T^{0}_{2}(U)}
.
Thus
d{\underline{\underline{\tau}}}={\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}}\tau_{ij|k}e^{i}\otimes e^{j}\otimes e^{k}
,
and there are no indices to contract to satisfy Einstein convention:
There is no objective divergence of 0 2 tensors.

S.10
Euclidean framework and “classic divergence” of a tensor (subjective)

Let
{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}
be a
C^{1}
tensor of order 2 of any kind.
An observer chooses a (Cartesian) Euclidean basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
and call
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
the associated Euclidean dot product.
And he calls
\sigma_{ij}
the components of
{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}
,
e.g. writes
{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}.{\vec{e}}_{j}=\sum_{i}\sigma_{ij}{\vec{e}}_{i}
.

Definition S.31

(Usual divergence in classical mechanics.)
The divergence
{\rm div}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}
of
{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}
relative to the basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
,
is the column matrix (it is not a vector)

{\rm div}_{\!e}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}=\pmatrix{{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}{\partial\sigma_{1j}\over\partial x^{j}}\cr\vdots\cr{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}{\partial\sigma_{nj}\over\partial x^{j}}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\rm div}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}\quad\hbox{(a matrix)}.

(S.65)

(Take the divergences of the “row vectors” of
[{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|e}=[\sigma_{ij}]

to make the “column vector”
[{\rm div}_{e}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]
.)

Proposition S.32

The “so called vector”
{\rm div}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}
, in (
S.67
), is not a vector: It
does not satisfy the change of basis formula:
If
({\vec{a}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
are bases, if
P
is the transition matrix from
({\vec{a}}_{i})
to
({\vec{b}}_{i})
, if
[{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}=[A_{ij}]
and
[{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}=[B_{ij}]
,
with the divergence of
{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}
relative to
({\vec{a}}_{i})
and
({\vec{b}}_{i})
called
{\rm div}_{\!a}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}
and
{\rm div}_{\!b}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}
,
then neither a contravariant nor a covariant change of basis formula applies in general:

\hbox{neither}\quad[{\rm div}_{\!b}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}\neq P^{-1}.[{\rm div}_{\!a}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}\quad\hbox{nor}\quad[{\rm div}_{\!b}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}^{T}=[{\rm div}_{\!a}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}^{T}.P

(S.66)

(compare with (
S.62
)). So
{\rm div}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}
as given in (
S.67
) is neither a contravariant vector nor a covariant vector (it is just a matrix which depends on an observer).

Proof.
Consider the simple case
{\vec{b}}_{i}=\lambda{\vec{a}}_{i}
, for all
i
,
\lambda>1
: Transition matrix
P=\lambda I
, and
P^{-1}={1\over\lambda}I
.

For a
{1\choose 1}
tensor:

{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}=\sum_{ij}(\sigma_{b})^{i}_{j}{\vec{b}}_{i}\otimes b^{j}=\sum_{ij}(\sigma_{a})^{i}_{j}{\vec{a}}_{i}\otimes a^{j}
,
[{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}=P^{-1}.[{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}.P={1\over\lambda}.[{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}.\lambda=[{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}
,
i.e.
(\sigma_{a})^{i}_{j}=(\sigma_{b})^{i}_{j}
for all
i,j
.
Thus (
S.67
) gives

{\rm div}_{\!b}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}=\sum_{ij}(d(\sigma_{b})^{i}_{j}.{\vec{b}}_{j}){\vec{b}}_{i}=\sum_{ij}(d(\sigma_{a})^{i}_{j}.(\lambda{\vec{a}}_{j}))(\lambda{\vec{a}}_{i})=\lambda^{2}{\rm div}_{\!a}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}.

Thus
[{\rm div}_{\!b}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}\neq P^{-1}.[{\rm div}_{\!b}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}
and
[{\rm div}_{\!b}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}^{T}\neq[{\rm div}_{\!a}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}^{T}.P
.

For a
{0\choose 2}
tensor:

{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}=\sum_{ij}\sigma_{b,ij}b^{i}\otimes b^{j}=\sum_{ij}\sigma_{a,ij}a^{i}\otimes a^{j}
, and

[{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}=P^{T}.[{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}.P=\lambda^{2}[{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}
,
i.e.
\sigma_{b,ij}=\lambda^{2}\sigma_{a,ij}
for all
i,j
.
Thus (
S.67
) gives

{\rm div}_{\!b}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}=\sum_{ij}(d\sigma_{b,ij}.{\vec{b}}_{j}){\vec{b}}_{i}=\lambda^{2}\sum_{ij}(d\sigma_{a,ij}.(\lambda{\vec{a}}_{j}))(\lambda{\vec{a}}_{i})=\lambda^{4}{\rm div}_{\!a}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}.

Thus
[{\rm div}_{\!b}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}\neq P^{-1}.[{\rm div}_{\!b}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}
and
[{\rm div}_{\!b}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}^{T}\neq[{\rm div}_{\!a}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}^{T}.P
.

For a
{2\choose 0}
tensor:

{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}=\sum_{ij}\sigma_{b}^{ij}{\vec{b}}_{i}\otimes{\vec{b}}_{j}=\sum_{ij}\sigma_{a}^{ij}{\vec{a}}_{i}\otimes{\vec{a}}_{j}
, and

[{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}=P^{-T}.[{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}.P^{-1}={1\over\lambda^{2}}[{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}
,
i.e.
\sigma_{b}^{ij}={1\over\lambda^{2}}\sigma_{a}^{ij}
for all
i,j
.
Thus (
S.67
) gives

{\rm div}_{\!b}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}=\sum_{ij}(d\sigma_{b}^{ij}.{\vec{b}}_{j}){\vec{b}}_{i}={1\over\lambda^{2}}\sum_{ij}(d\sigma_{a}^{ij}.(\lambda{\vec{a}}_{j}))(\lambda{\vec{a}}_{i})={\rm div}_{\!a}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}.

Thus
[{\rm div}_{\!b}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}\neq P^{-1}.[{\rm div}_{\!b}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}
and
[{\rm div}_{\!b}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|{\vec{b}}}^{T}\neq[{\rm div}_{\!a}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}]_{|{\vec{a}}}^{T}.P
.

Remark: (
S.65
) can be written

{\rm div}{\underline{\underline{\sigma}}}=\sum_{ij}{\partial\sigma_{ij}\over\partial x_{j}}{\vec{E}}_{i}

(S.67)

where
({\vec{E}}_{i})
is the canonical basis in
{\cal M}_{\!n\!1}
the space of
n*1
column vectors.

Appendix T
Natural canonical isomorphisms

T.1
The adjoint of a linear map

Setting of §
A.12
:
E
and
F
are vector spaces,
{E^{*}}={\cal L}(E;\mathbb{R})
and
{F^{*}}={\cal L}(F;\mathbb{R})
are their dual spaces,
and the adjoint of a linear map
{\cal P}\in{\cal L}(E;F)
is the linear map
{\cal P}^{*}\in{\cal L}(F^{*};E^{*})
canonically defined by

\forall\ell\in{F^{*}},\quad{\cal P}^{*}(\ell):=\ell\circ{\cal P},\quad\hbox{written}\quad{\cal P}^{*}.\ell=\ell.{\cal P}

(T.1)

(dot notations
{\cal P}^{*}(\ell){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\cal P}^{*}.\ell
and
\ell\circ{\cal P}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\ell.{\cal P}
since
\ell
and
{\cal P}^{*}
are linear),
i.e., for all
(\ell,{\vec{u}})\in{F^{*}}\times E
,

{\cal P}^{*}(\ell)({\vec{u}})=\ell({\cal P}({\vec{u}})),\quad\hbox{written}\quad({\cal P}^{*}.\ell).{\vec{u}}=\ell.{\cal P}.{\vec{u}}.

(T.2)

Interpretation: If
{\cal P}
is the push-forward of vector fields, then
{\cal P}^{*}
is the pull-back of differential forms, see remark
7.5
.
In particular, it will be interpreted with
{\cal P}\in{{\cal L}_{i}}(E;F)
(linear and invertible = a change of observer).

T.2
An isomorphism
E\simeq E^{*}
is never natural (never objective)

Two observers
A
and
B
consider a linear map
L\in{\cal L}(E;E^{*})
;
Let
{\cal P}\in{\cal L}(E;E)
be the change of observer endomorphism.
Willing to work together,
A
and
B
(“naturally”) consider the diagram

\displaystyle{E\;\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\displaystyle\;L}\;}

\displaystyle{{}E^{*}\qquad\qquad\leftarrow\hbox{considered by observer $A$}}

\displaystyle{{\cal P}\downarrow\qquad\quad}

\displaystyle{{}\;\;\uparrow{\cal P}^{*}}

\displaystyle{E\;\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{\displaystyle\;L}\;}

\displaystyle{{}E^{*}\qquad\qquad\leftarrow\hbox{considered by observer $B$}}

(T.3)

Definition T.1

(Spivak
[
19
]
.) A linear map
L\in{\cal L}(E;E^{*})
is natural iff the diagram (
T.3
) commutes for all
{\cal P}\in{\cal L}(E;E)
:

\hbox{$L\in{\cal L}(E;E^{*})$ is natural}\;\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\;\forall{\cal P}\in{\cal L}(E;E),\;\;{\cal P}^{*}\circ L\circ{\cal P}=L.

(T.4)

(In that case, if
A
computes
L.{\vec{u}}
with the top line of the diagram, if
B
computes with the bottom line of the diagram, then they can easily check their results since here
L.{\vec{u}}=({\cal P}^{*}\circ L\circ{\cal P}).{\vec{u}}
.)

Question:
Does there exist an endomorphism
L
such that the diagram (
T.3
) commutes for all change of observers? That is, do we have

\exists?L\in{\cal L}(E;E),\;\forall{\cal P}\in{{\cal L}_{i}}(E;E),\quad{\cal P}^{*}\circ L\circ{\cal P}=L\;?

(T.5)

Answer:
Always
no
(if
L\neq 0
):

Theorem T.2

A (non-zero) linear map
L\in{\cal L}(E;E^{*})
is not natural: If
L\in{\cal L}(E;E^{*})-\{0\}
, then

\exists{\cal P}\in{{\cal L}_{i}}(E;E)\quad\hbox{s{.}t{.}}\quad L\neq{\cal P}^{*}\circ L\circ{\cal P}.

(T.6)

Proof.
(Spivak
[
19
]
.) It suffices to prove this proposition for
E=\vec{\mathbb{R}}
. Let
L\in{\cal L}(\vec{\mathbb{R}};(\vec{\mathbb{R}})^{*})
,
L\neq 0
.

Let
({\vec{a}}_{1})
be a basis in
\vec{\mathbb{R}}
(chosen by
A
). Let
({\vec{b}}_{1})
be a basis in
\vec{\mathbb{R}}
(chosen by
B
).

Consider
{\cal P}\in{{\cal L}_{i}}(\vec{\mathbb{R}};\vec{\mathbb{R}})
defined by
{\cal P}({\vec{a}}_{1})={\vec{b}}_{1}
(change of observer),
and let
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
s.t.
{\vec{b}}_{1}=\lambda{\vec{a}}_{1}
. Then
(
T.1
) gives

{\cal P}^{*}(\ell)({\vec{a}}_{1}):=\ell({\cal P}({\vec{a}}_{1}))=\ell({\vec{b}}_{1})=\ell(\lambda{\vec{a}}_{1})=\lambda\ell({\vec{a}}_{1})
,
thus
{\cal P}^{*}(\ell)=\lambda\ell
for all
\ell\in(\vec{\mathbb{R}})^{*}
.

Thus
{\cal P}^{*}(L({\cal P}({\vec{a}}_{1})))={\cal P}^{*}(L(\lambda{\vec{a}}_{1}))=\lambda{\cal P}^{*}(L({\vec{a}}_{1}))=\lambda^{2}L({\vec{a}}_{1})\neq L({\vec{a}}_{1})
when
\lambda^{2}\neq 1
. E.g.,
{\cal P}=2I
gives
L\neq{\cal P}^{*}\circ L\circ{\cal P}
(
=4L
), thus (
T.6
):
A (non-zero) linear map
E\rightarrow E^{*}
cannot be natural.

Example T.3

Consider
E
s.t.
\dim E=1
, and consider the linear map
L\in{\cal L}(E;E^{*})
which sends a basis
({\vec{a}}_{1})
onto its dual basis
(\pi_{a1})
, so
L
is defined by
L.{\vec{a}}_{1}:=\pi_{a1}
.

Question: If
({\vec{b}}_{1})
is another basis,
\lambda\neq\pm 1
and
{\vec{b}}_{1}=\lambda{\vec{a}}_{1}
(change of unit of measurement), does
L.{\vec{b}}_{1}=\pi_{b1}
, i.e. does
L
also sends
({\vec{b}}_{1})
onto its dual basis?

Answer: No. Indeed,

{\vec{b}}_{1}=\lambda{\vec{a}}_{1}
gives
\pi_{b1}={1\over\lambda}\pi_{a1}
, thus

L.{\vec{b}}_{1}=\lambda L.{\vec{a}}_{1}=\lambda\pi_{a1}=\lambda^{2}\pi_{b1}\neq\pi_{b1}
since
\lambda^{2}\neq 1
.
In words:
L
is not natural, cf. (
T.6
).

A different presentation:
Let
L_{A}
and
L_{B}
be defined by
L_{A}.{\vec{a}}_{j}=\pi_{aj}
and
L_{B}.{\vec{b}}_{j}=\pi_{bj}
for all
j
.
And suppose that
{\vec{b}}_{j}=\lambda{\vec{a}}_{j}
for all
j
.
Then,
L_{A}.{\vec{b}}_{j}=\lambda L_{A}.{\vec{a}}_{j}=\lambda\pi_{aj}=\lambda^{2}\pi_{bj}=\lambda^{2}L_{B}.{\vec{b}}_{j}\neq L_{B}.{\vec{b}}_{j}
when
\lambda^{2}\neq 1
, that is,
L_{A}\neq L_{B}
when
\lambda^{2}\neq 1
: An operator that sends a basis onto its dual basis is not natural.

Example T.4

Let
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
be an inner dot product in
E={\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
. Let
{\vec{R}}_{g}\in{\cal L}(E^{*};E)
be the Riesz representation map, that is, defined by
{\vec{R}}_{g}(\ell)={\vec{\ell}}_{g}
where
{\vec{\ell}}_{g}
is defined by

({\vec{\ell}}_{g},{\vec{v}})_{g}=\ell.{\vec{v}}
for all
{\vec{v}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
, cf (
F.3
).

Question: Is
{\vec{R}}_{g}
natural?

Answer: No: Consider the diagram

\Bigl(\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr E^{*}\;\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\displaystyle{\vec{R}}_{g}}\;&E\cr\vskip-6.0pt\cr{\cal P}^{*}\downarrow\qquad\quad&\;\uparrow{\cal P}\cr\vskip-4.0pt\cr E^{*}\;\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{\displaystyle{\vec{R}}_{g}}\;&E\cr}}\,\Bigr)

with
{\cal P}=\lambda I
,
\lambda\neq\pm 1
. Then
{\cal P}^{*}=\lambda I
, and
{\cal P}.{\vec{R}}_{g}.{\cal P}^{*}.\ell=\lambda^{2}{\vec{R}}_{g}.\ell\neq{\vec{R}}_{g}.\ell
gives
{\cal P}.{\vec{R}}_{g}.{\cal P}^{*}\neq{\vec{R}}_{g}
:
So
{\vec{R}}_{g}
is not natural, cf. (
T.6
).
(You may prefer to consider the diagram (
T.3
) with
L={\vec{R}}_{g}^{-1}
.)

A different presentation:
Consider two distinct Euclidean dot products
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
(e.g., built with a foot and built with a metre).
So
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}=\lambda^{2}(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
with
\lambda^{2}\neq 1
.
Let
{\vec{R}}_{g},{\vec{R}}_{h}\in{\cal L}({{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{*}};{\mathbb{R}^{n}})
be the Riesz operators relative to
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{h}
, that is
{\vec{R}}_{g}.\ell={\vec{\ell}}_{g}
and
{\vec{R}}_{h}.\ell={\vec{\ell}}_{h}
are given by
\ell.{\vec{v}}=({\vec{\ell}}_{g},{\vec{v}})_{g}=({\vec{\ell}}_{h},{\vec{v}})_{h}
for all
{\vec{v}}\in{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
.
We have
{\vec{\ell}}_{h}=\lambda^{2}{\vec{\ell}}_{g}
, cf. (
F.11
), thus
{\vec{R}}_{h}=\lambda^{2}{\vec{R}}_{g}\neq{\vec{R}}_{g}
since
\lambda^{2}\neq 1
:
A Riesz representation operator is not natural (it is observer dependent).

T.3
Natural canonical isomorphism
E\simeq E^{**}

Two observers
A
and
B
consider the same linear map
L\in{\cal L}(E;{E^{**}})
(where
{E^{**}}=(E^{*})^{*}={\cal L}({E^{*}};\mathbb{R})
).
Willing to work together, they (“naturally”) consider the diagram

\displaystyle{E\;\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\displaystyle L}\;}

\displaystyle{{}{E^{**}}\qquad\qquad\leftarrow\hbox{considered by observer $A$}}

\displaystyle{{\cal P}\downarrow\qquad\quad}

\displaystyle{{}\;\;\downarrow{{\cal P}^{**}}}

\displaystyle{E\;\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{\displaystyle L}\;}

\displaystyle{{}{E^{**}}\qquad\qquad\leftarrow\hbox{considered by observer $B$}}

(T.7)

where
{\cal P}\in{\cal L}(E;E)
is a linear diffeomorphism,
{\cal P}^{*}\in{\cal L}(E^{*};E^{*})
its adjoint,
given by
{\cal P}^{*}(\ell)=\ell\circ{\cal P}
cf. (
T.1
),
and
{{\cal P}^{**}}\in{{\cal L}_{i}}({E^{**}};{E^{**}})
the adjoint of
{\cal P}^{*}
, thus
given by
{{\cal P}^{**}}(u)=u\circ{\cal P}^{*}
for all
u\in{E^{**}}
cf. (
T.1
),
i.e.
{{\cal P}^{**}}
is given by, for all
(\ell,u)\in E^{*}\times{E^{**}}
,

({{\cal P}^{**}}(u))(\ell)=u(\ell\circ{\cal P}),\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad({{\cal P}^{**}}.u).\ell=u.(\ell.{\cal P}).

(T.8)

Question:
Does there exist a linear map
L\in{\cal L}(E;{E^{**}})
that is natural?

Answer:
Yes (particular case of the next proposition):

Proposition T.5

The canonical isomorphism

{\cal J}_{E}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr E&\rightarrow{E^{**}}\cr{\vec{u}}&\rightarrow u={\cal J}_{E}({\vec{u}})\quad\hbox{defined by}\quad{\cal J}_{E}({\vec{u}})(\ell):=\ell.{\vec{u}},\quad\forall\ell\in{E^{*}},\crcr}}\,\right.

(T.9)

is natural, that is,
F
being another finite dimensional vector space, the diagram

\displaystyle{E\;\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\displaystyle{\cal J}_{E}}\;}

\displaystyle{{}{E^{**}}}

\displaystyle{{\cal P}\downarrow\qquad\quad}

\displaystyle{{}\;\;\downarrow{{\cal P}^{**}}}

\displaystyle{F\;\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{\displaystyle{\cal J}_{F}}\;}

\displaystyle{{}{F^{**}}}

written

\displaystyle{E\;\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\displaystyle{\cal J}}\;}

\displaystyle{{}{E^{**}}}

\displaystyle{{\cal P}\downarrow\qquad\quad}

\displaystyle{{}\;\;\downarrow{{\cal P}^{**}}}

\displaystyle{F\;\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{\displaystyle{\cal J}}\;}

\displaystyle{{}{F^{**}}}

(T.10)

commutes for all
{\cal P}\in{\cal L}(E;F)
, i.e.

\forall{\cal P}\in{\cal L}(E;F),\quad{{\cal P}^{**}}\circ{\cal J}_{E}={\cal J}_{F}\circ{\cal P},\quad\hbox{and we write}\quad E\simeq{E^{**}}.

(T.11)

Thus we can use the unambiguous notation (observer independent)

{\cal J}({\vec{u}}){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{u}},\quad\hbox{and}\quad{\cal J}({\vec{u}}).\ell{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}{\vec{u}}.\ell\quad(=\ell.{\vec{u}}).

(T.12)

(And
u={\cal J}({\vec{u}})
is the derivation operator in the direction
{\vec{u}}
.)

Proof.
(Spivak
[
19
]
.)
It is trivial that
{\cal J}_{E}
is linear and bijective (
E
is finite dimensional): It is an isomorphism.
Then

\displaystyle({\cal P}^{**}\circ{\cal J}_{E}({\vec{u}}))(\ell)\mathop{=}^{(\ref{defadjLP2})}{\cal J}_{E}({\vec{u}})(\ell.{\cal P})\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqisomcan0})}(\ell\circ{\cal P})({\vec{u}})=\ell({\cal P}({\vec{u}}))\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqisomcan0})}{\cal J}_{F}({\cal P}({\vec{u}}))(\ell)
, for all
\ell\in F^{*}
and all
{\vec{u}}\in E
,
thus
{\cal P}^{**}\circ{\cal J}_{E}({\vec{u}})={\cal J}_{F}({\cal P}({\vec{u}}))
, for all
{\vec{u}}\in E
, thus
{\cal P}^{**}\circ{\cal J}_{E}={\cal J}_{F}\circ{\cal P}
.

Proposition T.6

(Characterization of
{\cal J}_{E}
.)
{\cal J}_{E}
sends any basis
({\vec{a}}_{i})
onto its bidual basis.
(Expected, since
{\cal J}_{E}({\vec{u}})
is the directional derivative in the direction
{\vec{u}}
, whatever
{\vec{u}}
.)

Proof.
Let
({\vec{a}}_{i})
be a basis and
(\pi_{ai})
be its dual basis (defined by
\pi_{ai}.{\vec{a}}_{j}=\delta_{ij}
for all
i,j
).
Then (
T.9
) gives

{\cal J}_{E}({\vec{a}}_{j}).\pi_{ai}=\pi_{ai}.{\vec{a}}_{j}=\delta_{ij}
for all
i,j
,
thus
({\cal J}_{E}({\vec{a}}_{j}))
is the dual basis of
(\pi_{ai})
, i.e., is the bidual basis of
({\vec{a}}_{i})
;
True for all basis:
{\cal J}_{E}({\vec{b}}_{j}).\pi_{bi}=\pi_{bi}.{\vec{b}}_{j}=\delta_{ij}
for all
i,j
.

T.4
Natural canonical isomorphisms
{\cal L}(E;F)\simeq{\cal L}(F^{*},E;\mathbb{R})\simeq{\cal L}(E^{*};F^{*})

E,F,A,B
are finite dimensional vector spaces.
Consider the canonical isomorphism
