{{\cal J}_{\!E\!F}}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\cal L}(E;F)&\rightarrow{\cal L}({F^{*}},E;\mathbb{R})\cr L&\rightarrow{\widetilde{L}}={{\cal J}_{\!E\!F}}(L)\quad\hbox{where}\quad{\widetilde{L}}(\ell,{\vec{u}}):=\ell.L.{\vec{u}},\quad\forall(\ell,{\vec{u}})\in F^{*}\times E.\crcr}}\,\right.

(T.13)

Let
{\cal P}_{1}\in{{\cal L}_{i}}(E;A)
and
{\cal P}_{2}\in{\cal L}(F;B)
, and consider the diagram

\displaystyle{{\cal L}(E;F)\;\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\displaystyle{{\cal J}_{\!E\!F}}}\;}

\displaystyle{{}{\cal L}(F^{*},E;\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle{{{\cal I}_{\cal P}}\downarrow\qquad\quad\hphantom{\;\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{\displaystyle L}\;}}

\displaystyle{{}\qquad\qquad\downarrow\widetilde{{\cal I}_{\cal P}}}

\displaystyle{{\cal L}(A;B)\;\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{\displaystyle{{\cal J}_{\!A\!B}}}\;}

\displaystyle{{}{\cal L}(B^{*},A;\mathbb{R})}

(T.14)

where

{{\cal I}_{\cal P}}(L)={\cal P}_{2}.L.{\cal P}_{1}^{-1}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\widetilde{{\cal I}_{\cal P}}({\widetilde{L}})(b,{\vec{a}})={\widetilde{L}}(b.{\cal P}_{2},{\cal P}_{1}^{-1}.{\vec{a}})\quad\forall(b,{\vec{a}})\in B^{*}\times A.

(T.15)

(
{{\cal I}_{\cal P}}
and
\widetilde{{\cal I}_{\cal P}}
are the push-forwards for linear maps
L\in{\cal L}(E;F)
and for bilinear forms
{\widetilde{L}}\in{\cal L}(F^{*},E;\mathbb{R})
.)

Proposition T.7

The canonical isomorphism
{{\cal J}_{\!E\!F}}
is natural, that is, the diagram (
T.14
) commutes for all

{\cal P}_{1}\in{{\cal L}_{i}}(E,A)
and all
{\cal P}_{2}\in{\cal L}(F,B)
:

\widetilde{{\cal I}_{\cal P}}\circ{{\cal J}_{\!E\!F}}={{\cal J}_{\!A\!B}}\circ{{\cal I}_{\cal P}},\quad\hbox{and}\quad{\cal L}(E;F)\mathop{\simeq}^{natural}{\cal L}(F^{*},E;\mathbb{R}).

(T.16)

Thus
\displaystyle{\cal L}(E^{*};F^{*})\mathop{\simeq}^{natural}{\cal L}(E;F)
.

Proof.
\displaystyle{{\cal J}_{\!A\!B}}({{\cal I}_{\cal P}}(L))(b,{\vec{a}})

\displaystyle\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdefJ2g})}b.{{\cal I}_{\cal P}}(L).{\vec{a}}

\displaystyle\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdiagcalj2g2})}b.({\cal P}_{2}.L.{\cal P}_{1}^{-1}).{\vec{a}}

=(b.{\cal P}_{2}).L.({\cal P}_{1}^{-1}.{\vec{a}})

\displaystyle\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdefJ2g})}{{\cal J}_{\!E\!F}}(L)(b.{\cal P}_{2},{\cal P}_{1}^{-1}.{\vec{a}})

\displaystyle\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqdiagcalj2g2})}\widetilde{{\cal I}_{\cal P}}({{\cal J}_{\!E\!F}}(L))(b,{\vec{a}})
, true for all
L\in{\cal L}(E;F)
,
b\in B^{*}
,
{\vec{a}}\in A
, thus (
T.16
).

Thus
\displaystyle{\cal L}({E^{*}};{F^{*}}){\mathop{\simeq}^{(\ref{eqtJ2f})}}{\cal L}(({F^{*}})^{*},{E^{*}};\mathbb{R}){\mathop{\simeq}^{(\ref{eqpropJnat0})}}{\cal L}(F,{E^{*}};\mathbb{R}){\mathop{\simeq}^{(\ref{eqtJ2f})}}{\cal L}({E^{**}};F){\mathop{\simeq}^{(\ref{eqpropJnat0})}}{\cal L}(E;F)
.

Consider the canonical isomorphism (defines the transposed of a bilinear map)

{\cal K}_{\!\!E\!F}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\cal L}(E,F;\mathbb{R})&\rightarrow{\cal L}(F,E;\mathbb{R})\cr T&\rightarrow{\cal K}_{\!\!E\!F}(T)\crcr}}\,\right\},\quad{\cal K}_{\!\!E\!F}(T)({\vec{u}},{\vec{v}}):=T({\vec{v}},{\vec{u}}),\quad\forall({\vec{u}},{\vec{v}})\in E\times F,

(T.17)

and
Z_{\!A\!B}\in{\cal L}(E,F;\mathbb{R})\rightarrow{\cal L}(A,B;\mathbb{R})
defined by

Z_{\!A\!B}(T)({\vec{a}},{\vec{b}}):=T({\cal P}_{1}^{-1}.{\vec{a}},{\cal P}_{2}^{-1}.{\vec{b}})
for all
({\vec{a}},{\vec{b}})\in A\times B
.

Proposition T.8

The canonical isomorphism
{\cal K}_{\!\!E\!F}
is natural:
For all
({\cal P}_{1},{\cal P}_{2})\in{{\cal L}_{i}}(E;A)\times{\cal L}(F;B)
,
the diagram

\displaystyle{{\cal L}(E,F;\mathbb{R})\;\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\displaystyle{\cal K}_{\!\!E\!F}}\;}

\displaystyle{{}{\cal L}(F,E;\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle{Z_{\!A\!B}\;\downarrow\qquad\quad\hphantom{\;\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{\displaystyle L}\;}}

\displaystyle{{}\qquad\quad\downarrow Z_{\!B\!A}}

\displaystyle{{\cal L}(A,B;\mathbb{R})\;\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{\displaystyle{\cal K}_{\!\!A\!B}}\;}

\displaystyle{{}{\cal L}(B,A;\mathbb{R})}

commutes:
\displaystyle{\cal L}(E,F;\mathbb{R})\mathop{\simeq}^{natural}{\cal L}(F,E;\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.
{\cal K}_{\!\!E\!F}(Z_{\!A\!B}(T))({\vec{b}},{\vec{a}})=Z_{\!A\!B}(T)({\vec{a}},{\vec{b}})=T({\cal P}_{2}^{-1}.{\vec{b}},{\cal P}_{1}^{-1}.{\vec{a}})

and

Z_{\!B\!A}({\cal K}_{\!\!E\!F}(T))({\vec{a}},{\vec{b}})={\cal K}_{\!\!E\!F}(T)({\cal P}_{1}^{-1}.{\vec{a}},{\cal P}_{2}^{-1}.{\vec{b}})=T({\cal P}_{2}^{-1}.{\vec{b}},{\cal P}_{1}^{-1}.{\vec{a}})
,
thus
{\cal K}_{\!\!A\!B}\circ Z_{\!A\!B}=Z_{\!B\!A}\circ{\cal K}_{\!\!E\!F}
.

T.5
Natural canonical isomorphisms
{\cal L}(E;{\cal L}(E;F))\simeq{\cal L}(E,E;F)\simeq{\cal L}(F^{*},E,E;\mathbb{R})

For application to the second order derivative
d(d{\vec{u}})\simeq d^{2}{\vec{u}}
and, with
{\vec{u}}\in{T^{1}_{0}(U)}
, the notation
d{\vec{u}}\in{T^{1}_{1}(U)}
, then
d^{2}{\vec{u}}\in{T^{1}_{2}(U)}
, …,
d^{k}{\vec{u}}\in T^{1}_{k}(U)
, …

Consider the canonical isomorphism

{\cal J}_{12E}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\cal L}(E;{\cal L}(E;F))&\rightarrow{\cal L}(E,E;F)\cr T_{1}&\rightarrow T_{2}={\cal J}_{12E}(T_{1})\crcr}}\,\right\},\quad{\cal J}_{12E}(T_{1})({\vec{u}}_{1},{\vec{u}}_{2}):=T_{1}({\vec{u}}_{1}).{\vec{u}}_{2}\in F,\quad\forall{\vec{u}}_{1},{\vec{u}}_{2}\in E,

(T.18)

and the canonical isomorphism

{\cal J}_{23E}:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{\cal L}(E,E;F)&\rightarrow{\cal L}(F^{*},E,E;\mathbb{R})\cr T_{2}&\rightarrow{\cal J}_{23E}(T_{2})=T_{3}\crcr}}\,\right\},\quad T_{3}(\ell,{\vec{u}},{\vec{v}}):=\ell.T_{2}({\vec{u}}_{1},{\vec{u}}_{2}),\quad\forall{\vec{u}}_{1},{\vec{u}}_{2}\in E,\;\forall\ell\in F^{*}.

(T.19)

Proposition T.9

{\cal J}_{12}
and
{\cal J}_{23}
are natural. Thus
{\cal J}_{23}\circ{\cal J}_{12}
is natural.

Proof.
1- We have to prove that the following diagram commutes:

\displaystyle{{\cal L}(E;{\cal L}(E;F))\;\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\displaystyle{\cal J}_{12E}}\;}

\displaystyle{{}{\cal L}(E,E;F)}

\displaystyle{Z_{\!A\!B}\downarrow\qquad\qquad\qquad}

\displaystyle{{}\qquad\downarrow Y_{\!A\!B}}

\displaystyle{{\cal L}(A;{\cal L}(A;B))\;\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\displaystyle{\cal J}_{12A}}\;}

\displaystyle{{}{\cal L}(A,A;B)}

where

\displaystyle{{}Z_{\!A\!B}(T_{1})({\vec{a}}_{1}).{\vec{a}}_{2}:=T_{1}({\cal P}_{1}^{-1}.{\vec{a}}_{1}).({\cal P}_{1}^{-1}.{\vec{a}}_{2}),}

\displaystyle{{}Y_{\!A\!B}(T_{2})({\vec{a}}_{1},{\vec{a}}_{2})=T_{2}({\cal P}_{1}^{-1}.{\vec{a}}_{1},{\cal P}_{1}^{-1}.{\vec{a}}_{2}),}

(T.20)

(the “push-forwards)
for all
{\vec{a}}_{1},{\vec{a}}_{2}\in A
and
L_{AB}\in{\cal L}(A;B)
.

Let
T_{1}\in{\cal L}(E;{\cal L}(E;F))
. We have

{\cal J}_{12A}(Z_{\!A\!B}(T_{1}))({\vec{a}}_{1}).{\vec{a}}_{2}=Z_{\!A\!B}(T_{1})({\vec{a}}_{1}).{\vec{a}}_{2}=T_{1}({\cal P}_{1}^{-1}.{\vec{a}}_{1}).({\cal P}_{1}^{-1}.{\vec{a}}_{2})
, and

Y_{\!A\!B}({\cal J}_{12E}(T_{1}))({\vec{a}}_{1},{\vec{a}}_{2})={\cal J}_{12E}(T_{1})({\cal P}_{1}^{-1}.{\vec{a}}_{1},{\cal P}_{1}^{-1}.{\vec{a}}_{2})=T_{1}({\cal P}_{1}^{-1}.{\vec{a}}_{1}).({\cal P}_{1}^{-1}.{\vec{a}}_{2})
,

thus
{\cal J}_{12A}\circ Z_{\!A\!B}=Y_{\!A\!B}\circ{\cal J}_{12E}
, thus
{\cal J}_{12}
is natural.

2- We have to prove that the following diagram commutes:

\displaystyle{{\cal L}(E,E;F)\;\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\displaystyle{\cal J}_{23E}}\;}

\displaystyle{{}{\cal L}(F^{*},E,E;\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle{Z_{\!A\!B}\downarrow\qquad\qquad\qquad}

\displaystyle{{}\qquad\downarrow Y_{\!A\!B}}

\displaystyle{{\cal L}(A,A;B)\;\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\displaystyle{\cal J}_{23A}}\;}

\displaystyle{{}{\cal L}(B^{*},A,A;\mathbb{R})}

where

\displaystyle{{}\ell_{B}.Z_{\!A\!B}(T_{2})({\vec{a}}_{1},{\vec{a}}_{2}):=(\ell_{B}.{\cal P}_{2}).T_{2}({\cal P}_{1}^{-1}.{\vec{a}}_{1},{\cal P}_{1}^{-1}.{\vec{a}}_{2}),}

\displaystyle{{}Y_{\!A\!B}(T_{3})(\ell_{B},{\vec{a}}_{1},{\vec{a}}_{2})=T_{3}(\ell_{B}.{\cal P}_{2},{\cal P}_{1}^{-1}.{\vec{a}}_{1},{\cal P}_{1}^{-1}.{\vec{a}}_{2}),}

(T.21)

(the “push-forwards)
for all
{\vec{a}}_{1},{\vec{a}}_{2}\in A
and
\ell_{B}\in B^{*}
.

Let
T_{2}\in{\cal L}(E,E;F)
. We have

{\cal J}_{23A}(\ell_{B},Z_{\!A\!B}(T_{2})({\vec{a}}_{1},{\vec{a}}_{2}))=\ell_{B}.Z_{\!A\!B}(T_{2})({\vec{a}}_{1},{\vec{a}}_{2})=(\ell_{B}.{\cal P}_{2}).T_{2}({\cal P}_{1}^{-1}.{\vec{a}}_{1},{\cal P}_{1}^{-1}.{\vec{a}}_{2})
, and

Y_{\!A\!B}({\cal J}_{23A}(T_{2}))(\ell_{B},{\vec{a}}_{1},{\vec{a}}_{2})={\cal J}_{23A}(T_{2})(\ell_{B}.{\cal P}_{2},{\cal P}_{1}^{-1}.{\vec{a}}_{1},{\cal P}_{1}^{-1}.{\vec{a}}_{2})=\ell_{B}.{\cal P}_{2}.T_{2}({\cal P}_{1}^{-1}.{\vec{a}}_{1},{\cal P}_{1}^{-1}.{\vec{a}}_{2})

thus
{\cal J}_{23A}\circ Z_{\!A\!B}=Y_{\!A\!B}\circ{\cal J}_{23E}
, thus
{\cal J}_{23}
is natural.

Appendix U
Distribution in brief: A covariant concept

We refer to the books of Laurent Schwartz for a full description.
In continuum mechanics, with
\Omega
an open set in
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
and
for the space of the finite energy functions
{L^{2}(\Omega)}

and its sub-spaces, a distribution gives a covariant formulation for the virtual power, as used by Germain.

U.1
Definitions

Usual notations:
Let
p\in[1,\infty[
(e.g.
p=2
for finite energy functions), and let

{L^{p}(\Omega)}:=\{f:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}:\int_{\Omega}|f(x)|^{p}\,d\Omega<\infty\}\quad\hbox{and}\quad||f||_{p}=(\int_{\Omega}|f(x)|^{p}\,d\Omega)^{1\over p},

(U.1)

the space of functions such that
|f|^{p}
is Lebesgue integrable with
||.||_{p}
its usual norm. Then
({L^{p}(\Omega)},||.||_{L^{p}})
is a Banach space (a complete normed space). And let

{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}:=\{f:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}:\sup_{x\in\Omega}(|f(x)|)<\infty\},\quad\hbox{and}\quad||f||_{\infty}=\sup_{x\in\Omega}(|f(x)|),

(U.2)

the space of Lebesgue measurable bounded functions with
||.||_{\infty}
its usual norm. Then
({L^{\infty}(\Omega)},||.||_{L^{\infty}})
is a Banach space (a complete normed space).

Definition U.1

If
f\in{\cal F}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
, then its support is the set

{\rm supp}(f):=\overline{\{x\in\Omega:f(x)\neq 0\}}=\hbox{the closure of }\{x\in\Omega:f(x)\neq 0\}

(U.3)

= the set where it is interesting to study
f
.

The closure is required:
E.g., if
\Omega=]0,2\pi[
and
f(x)=\sin x
for all
x\in\omega
, then
\{x\in\Omega:f(x)\neq 0\}=]0,\pi[\cup]\pi,2\pi[
;
And the point
\pi
is a point of interest since
\sin
varies in its vicinity:
f^{\prime}(\pi)=\cos(\pi)=-1\neq 0
;
So it is the closure
{\rm supp}(f):=\overline{]0,\pi[\cup]\pi,2\pi[}=[0,2\pi]
that is considered.

Definition U.2

(Schwartz notation,
D
being the letter after
C
:)
Let

{{\cal D}(\Omega)}:=C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})=\{\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})\hbox{ s{.}t{.}\ ${\rm supp}(\varphi)$ is compact in $\Omega$}\}.

(U.4)

E.g.,
\Omega=\mathbb{R}
,
\varphi(x):=e^{-{1\over 1-x^{2}}}
if
x\in]-1,1[
and
\varphi(x):=0
elsewhere:

\varphi\in{{\cal D}(\mathbb{R})}
with
{\rm supp}(\varphi)=[-1,1]
.

And
{{\cal D}(\Omega)}
is a vector space which is dense in
({L^{p}(\Omega)},||.||_{L^{p}})
for any
p\in[1,\infty[
.

Definition U.3

A distribution in
\Omega
is a linear
{{\cal D}(\Omega)}
-continuous
3
3

3

The
{{\cal D}(\Omega)}
-continuity of
T
is defined by: 1- A sequence
(\varphi_{n})_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}
in
{{\cal D}(\Omega)}
converges in
{{\cal D}(\Omega)}
towards a function
\varphi\in{{\cal D}(\Omega)}

iff there exists a compact
K\subset\Omega
s.t.
{\rm supp}(\varphi_{n})\subset K
for all
n
, and

||{\partial^{k}\varphi\over\partial x_{i_{1}}...\partial x_{i_{k}}}-{\partial^{k}\varphi_{n}\over\partial x_{i_{1}}...\partial x_{i_{k}}}||_{\infty}\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}0
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and all
i_{j}
; 2-
T
is continuous at
\varphi\in{{\cal D}(\Omega)}
iff
\displaystyle T(\varphi_{n})\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}T(\varphi)
for any sequence
\displaystyle(\varphi_{n})_{\mathbb{N}}\in{{\cal D}(\Omega)}^{\mathbb{N}}\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\varphi
in
{{\cal D}(\Omega)}
.

function

T:\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr{{\cal D}(\Omega)}&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cr\varphi&\rightarrow T(\varphi){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\langle T,\varphi\rangle\crcr}}\,\right.

(U.5)

The space of distribution in
\Omega
is named
{{\cal D}^{\prime}(\Omega)}
(the dual of
{{\cal D}(\Omega)}
).

The notation
\langle T,\varphi\rangle_{{{\cal D}^{\prime}(\Omega)},{{\cal D}(\Omega)}}=\langle T,\varphi\rangle
is the “duality bracket” = the “covariance–contravariance bracket” between a linear function
T\in{{\cal D}^{\prime}(\Omega)}
and a vector
\varphi\in{{\cal D}(\Omega)}
.

Definition U.4

Let
f\in{L^{p}(\Omega)}
. The regular distribution
T_{f}\in{{\cal D}^{\prime}(\Omega)}
associated to
f
is defined by

T_{f}(\varphi):=\int_{\Omega}f(x)\varphi(x)\,d\Omega,\quad\forall\varphi\in{{\cal D}(\Omega)}.

(U.6)

So
T_{f}
is a measuring instrument with density
dm_{f}(x)=f(x)\,d\Omega
, i.e.
T_{f}(\varphi):=\int_{\Omega}\varphi(x)\,dm_{f}(x)
.

Definition U.5

Let
x_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
. The Dirac measure at
{x_{0}}
is the distribution
\displaystyle T{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\delta_{x_{0}}\in{{\cal D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})}
defined by,
for all
\varphi\in{{\cal D}(\mathbb{R})}
,

\delta_{x_{0}}(\varphi)=\varphi({x_{0}}),\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad\langle\delta_{x_{0}},\varphi\rangle=\varphi({x_{0}}).

(U.7)

And
\delta_{x_{0}}
is not a regular distribution (
\delta_{x_{0}}
is not a density measure): There is no integrable function
f
such that
T_{f}=\delta_{x_{0}}
.
Interpretation:
\delta_{x_{0}}
corresponds to an ideal measuring device: The precision is perfect at
{x_{0}}
(gives the exact value
\varphi({x_{0}})
at
{x_{0}}
). In real life
\delta_{x_{0}}
is the ideal approximation of
T_{f_{n}}
where
f_{n}
is e.g. given by
f_{n}(x)=n1_{[{x_{0}},{x_{0}}+{1\over n}]}
(drawing):
For all
\varphi\in{{\cal D}(\Omega)}
,
T_{f_{n}}(\varphi)\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\delta_{x_{0}}(\varphi)=\varphi({x_{0}})
.

Generalization of the definition:
In (
U.5
)
{{\cal D}(\Omega)}=C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})

is replaced by
C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega;{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})
. So if you consider a basis
({\vec{e}}_{i})
then
{\vec{\varphi}}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega;{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})
reads
{\vec{\varphi}}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\varphi^{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
with
\varphi^{i}\in{{\cal D}(\Omega)}
for all
i
.

Example U.6

Power: Let
\alpha:\Omega\rightarrow{T^{0}_{1}(\Omega)}
be a differential form. Then

P=T_{\alpha}
defined by
P({\vec{v}})=\int_{\Omega}\alpha.{\vec{v}}\,d\Omega
gives the virtual power associated to
\alpha
relative to the vector field
{\vec{v}}
(mechanics and thermodynamics).

U.2
Derivation of a distribution

Let
O
be a point in
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
(an origin). If
p\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
and if
({\vec{e}}_{i})
is a basis in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
, let
{\vec{x}}=\overrightarrow{Op}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}x_{i}{\vec{e}}_{i}
.

Definition U.7

The derivative
{\partial T\over\partial x_{i}}
of a distribution
T\in{{\cal D}^{\prime}(\Omega)}
is the distribution
\in{{\cal D}^{\prime}(\Omega)}
defined by, for all
\varphi\in{{\cal D}(\Omega)}
,

{\partial T\over\partial x_{i}}(\varphi):=-T({\partial\varphi\over\partial x_{i}}),\quad\hbox{i.e.}\quad\langle{\partial T\over\partial x_{i}},\varphi\rangle:=-\langle T,{\partial\varphi\over\partial x_{i}}\rangle.

(U.8)

(
{\partial T\over\partial x_{i}}
is indeed a distribution: Easy check.)

Example U.8

If
T=T_{f}
is a regular distribution with
f\in C^{1}(\Omega)
, then
{\partial(T_{f})\over\partial x_{i}}=T_{({\partial f\over\partial x_{i}})}
.
Indeed, for all
\varphi\in{{\cal D}(\Omega)}
,

{\partial(T_{f})\over\partial x_{i}}(\varphi)=-T_{f}({\partial\varphi\over\partial x_{i}})=-\int_{\Omega}f(x){\partial\varphi\over\partial x_{i}}\,d\Omega=+\int_{\Omega}{\partial f\over\partial x_{i}}\varphi(x)\,d\Omega+\int_{\Gamma}0\,d\Gamma
, since
\varphi
vanishes on
\Gamma=\partial\Omega
(the support of
\varphi
is compact in
\Omega
), thus

{\partial(T_{f})\over\partial x_{i}}(\varphi)=T_{({\partial f\over\partial x_{i}})}(\varphi)
for all
\varphi\in{{\cal D}(\Omega)}
.

Example U.9

Consider the Heaviside function (the unit step function)
{H_{0}}:={1_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}}
and the associated distribution

T=T_{H_{0}}
. Then
\langle(T_{H_{0}})^{\prime},\varphi\rangle:=-\langle T_{H_{0}},\varphi^{\prime}\rangle=-\int_{\Omega}{H_{0}}(x)\varphi^{\prime}(x)\,dx=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\varphi^{\prime}(x)\,dx=\varphi(0)=\langle\delta_{0},\varphi\rangle

for any
\varphi\in{{\cal D}(\mathbb{R})}
, thus
(T_{H_{0}})^{\prime}=\delta_{0}
. Written
{H_{0}}^{\prime}=\delta_{0}
in
{{\cal D}^{\prime}(\Omega)}
,
which is not in a equality between functions, because
{H_{0}}
is not derivable at
0
as a function, and
\delta_{0}
is not a function; It is equality between distributions: The notation
{H_{0}}^{\prime}
can only be used
to compute
{H_{0}}^{\prime}(\varphi)
(
=\langle{H_{0}}^{\prime},\varphi\rangle:=-\langle{H_{0}},\varphi^{\prime}\rangle
).

U.3
Hilbert space
{H^{1}(\Omega)}

U.3.1
Motivation

Consider the hat function

{\Lambda}(x)\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr=&x+1\hbox{ if }x\in[-1,0],\cr=&1-x\hbox{ if }x\in[0,1],\cr=&0\hbox{ otherwise }\crcr}}\,\right\}
(drawing).
When applying the finite element method, it is well-known that, if you use integrals (if you use the virtual power principle which makes you compute average values), then you can consider the derivative of the hat function
{\Lambda}
as if it was the usual derivative, i.e. at the points where the usual computation of
\Lambda^{\prime}
is meaningful, that is,

{\Lambda}^{\prime}(x)\left\{\hbox{}\,\vbox{\halign{\strut\hfil$\displaystyle{#}$ & $\displaystyle{{}#}$\hfil\cr=&1\hbox{ if }x\in]-1,0[,\cr=&{-}1\hbox{ if }x\in]0,1[,\cr=&0\hbox{ if }x\in\mathbb{R}-\{-1,0,1\}\crcr}}\,\right.

(U.9)

(drawing).

Problem:

\Lambda^{\prime}
is not defined at
-1,0,1
(the function
{\Lambda}
is not derivable at
-1,0,1
);

Question:

So does the “usual” computation
I=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Lambda^{\prime}(x)\varphi(x)\,dx
with (
U.9
) gives the good result? (This is not a trivial question: E.g., with
H_{0}=1_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
instead of
\Lambda
, we would get the absurd result
H_{0}^{\prime}=0
,
absurd since
H_{0}^{\prime}=\delta_{0}
.)

Answer:
Yes:

1- Consider
T_{\Lambda}
the regular distribution associated to
\Lambda
, cf. (
U.6
);

2- Then consider
(T_{\Lambda})^{\prime}
, cf. (
U.8
): We get

\displaystyle\langle(T_{\Lambda})^{\prime},\varphi\rangle\mathop{=}^{(\ref{eqTfp})}-\langle T_{\Lambda},\varphi^{\prime}\rangle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\Lambda}(x)\varphi^{\prime}(x)\,dx=-\int_{-1}^{0}{\Lambda}(x)\varphi^{\prime}(x)\,dx-\int_{0}^{1}{\Lambda}(x)\varphi^{\prime}(x)\,dx=+\int_{-1}^{0}1_{]-1,0[}(x)\varphi(x)\,dx+\int_{0}^{1}1_{]0,1[}\varphi(x)\,dx
,
for any
\varphi\in{\cal D}(\mathbb{R})
;

3- Thus
(T_{\Lambda})^{\prime}=T_{f}
where
f=1_{]-1,0[}+1_{]0,1[}
, that is
(T_{\Lambda})^{\prime}
is a regular distribution, And its is named
f={\Lambda}^{\prime}
within the distribution framework, i.e., for computations
\langle{\Lambda}^{\prime},\varphi\rangle:=\langle(T_{\Lambda})^{\prime},\varphi\rangle
with
\varphi\in{{\cal D}(\mathbb{R})}
(value
=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)\varphi(x)\,dx
).

U.3.2
Definition of
{H^{1}(\Omega)}

The space
C^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
is too small in many applications (e.g., for the
\Lambda
function above); We need a larger space where the functions are “derivable is a weaker sense” which is the distribution sense.
Consider a basis in
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
:

Definition U.10

The Sobolev space
{H^{1}(\Omega)}
is the subspace of
{L^{2}(\Omega)}
restricted to functions whose generalized derivatives are in
{L^{2}(\Omega)}
:

{H^{1}(\Omega)}=\{v\in{L^{2}(\Omega)}:{\partial v\over\partial x_{i}}\in{L^{2}(\Omega)},\;\forall i=1,...,n\}.

(U.10)

Usual shortened notation:
{H^{1}(\Omega)}=\{v\in{L^{2}(\Omega)}:{\vec{\rm grad}}v\in{{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{n}}\}
.

So to check that
v\in{H^{1}(\Omega)}
, even if
{\partial v\over\partial x_{i}}
does not exists in the classic way
(see the above hat function
\Lambda
), you have to:

1- Consider its associated regular distribution
T_{v}
,

2- Compute
{\partial T_{v}\over\partial x_{i}}
in
{{\cal D}^{\prime}(\Omega)}
,

3- And if, for all
i
, there exists
f_{i}\in{L^{2}(\Omega)}
s.t.
{\partial T_{v}\over\partial x_{i}}=T_{f_{i}}
,
then
v\in{H^{1}(\Omega)}
.

4- And then
f_{i}
is noted
{\partial v\over\partial x_{i}}
when used within the Lebesgue integrals

\int_{\Omega}{\partial v\over\partial x_{i}}(x)\varphi(x)\,dx
with
\varphi\in{{\cal D}(\Omega)}
.

E.g.,
{\Lambda}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
since
(T_{\Lambda})^{\prime}=T_{f}
with
f=1_{]-1,0[}+1_{]0,1[}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
; And
(T_{\Lambda})^{\prime}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\Lambda^{\prime}
(
=f
) in the distribution context (integral computations).

Let
(\cdot,\cdot)_{L^{2}}
and
||.||_{L^{2}}
be the usual inner dot product and norm in
{L^{2}(\Omega)}
, i.e.

(u,v)_{L^{2}}=\int_{\Omega}u(x)v(x)\,d\Omega,\quad\hbox{and}\quad||v||_{L^{2}}=\sqrt{(v,v)_{L^{2}}}=(\int_{\Omega}v(x)^{2}\,d\Omega)^{1\over 2}.

(U.11)

({L^{2}(\Omega)},(\cdot,\cdot)_{L^{2}})
is a Hilbert space. Then define, for all
u,v\in{H^{1}(\Omega)}
,

(u,v)_{H^{1}}=(u,v)_{L^{2}}+{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}({\partial u\over\partial x_{i}},{\partial v\over\partial x_{i}})_{L^{2}},\quad\hbox{and}\quad||v||_{H^{1}}=(v,v)_{H^{1}}^{1\over 2}.

(U.12)

Then
({H^{1}(\Omega)},(\cdot,\cdot)_{H^{1}})
is a Hilbert space (Riesz–Fisher theorem).

With a Euclidean dot product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
and a
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
-orthonormal basis,

(u,v)_{H^{1}}=(u,v)_{L^{2}}+({\vec{\rm grad}}u,{\vec{\rm grad}}v)_{L^{2}}.

(U.13)

U.3.3
Subspace
{H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)}
and its dual space
{H^{-1}(\Omega)}

The boundary
\Gamma=\partial\Omega
of
\Omega
is supposed to be regular. Let

{H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)}:=\{v\in{H^{1}(\Omega)}:v_{|\Gamma}=0\}.

(U.14)

Then
({H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)},(\cdot,\cdot)_{H^{1}})
is a Hilbert space.

More generally (without any regularity assumption on
\Gamma
),
{H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)}:=\overline{{{\cal D}(\Omega)}}^{H^{1}}=
the closure of
{{\cal D}(\Omega)}
in
({H^{1}(\Omega)},||.||_{H^{1}})
: This closure of
{{\cal D}(\Omega)}
in
{H^{1}(\Omega)}
enables the use of the distribution framework.

Notation : The dual space of
{H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)}
is the space

{H^{-1}(\Omega)}=({H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)})^{\prime}={\cal L}({H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)};\mathbb{R})

(U.15)

equipped with the (usual) norm

\displaystyle||T||_{H^{-1}}:=\sup_{||v||_{H^{1}_{0}}=1}|T(v)|
.
And (duality bracket), if
v\in{H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)}
and
T\in{H^{-1}(\Omega)}
then

T(v){\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\langle T,v\rangle_{{H^{-1}},{H^{1}_{0}}}{\;\mathop{=}^{\hbox{\footnotesize noted}}\;}\langle T,v\rangle.

(U.16)

Theorem U.11

(
Characterization of
{H^{-1}(\Omega)}=({H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)})^{\prime}
.) A distribution
T
is in
{H^{-1}(\Omega)}
iff

\exists(f,{\vec{g}})\in{L^{2}(\Omega)}\times{{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{n}}\quad\hbox{s{.}t{.}}\quad T=f-{\rm div}{\vec{g}}\;\;(\in{{\cal D}^{\prime}(\Omega)}),

(U.17)

that is, for all
v\in{H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)}
,

\langle T,v\rangle_{{H^{-1}},{H^{1}_{0}}}=\int_{\Omega}fv\,d\Omega+\int_{\Omega}dv.{\vec{g}}\,d\Omega.

(U.18)

And if
\Omega
is bounded then we can choose
f=0
.
If moreover
{\vec{g}}\in{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{n}
then

\langle T,v\rangle_{{H^{-1}},{H^{1}_{0}}}=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\,dx-\int_{\Omega}{\rm div}{\vec{g}}(x)v(x)\,dx.

(U.19)

(In fact we only need
{\vec{g}}\in H_{\rm div}(\Omega)=\{{\vec{g}}\in{{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{n}}:{\rm div}{\vec{g}}\in{L^{2}(\Omega)}\}
.)

Proof.
E.g., see Brezis
[
6
]
.

For boundary value problems with Neumann boundary conditions,
we then need
({H^{1}(\Omega)})^{\prime}
the dual space of
{H^{1}(\Omega)}
.
Characterization of
({H^{1}(\Omega)})^{\prime}
: We still have (
U.18
), but we have to replace (
U.17
) or (
U.19
) by, with a Euclidean dot product in
{\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
, see Brezis
[
6
]
,

\langle T,v\rangle_{({H^{1}})^{\prime},{H^{1}}}=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\,dx-\int_{\Omega}{\rm div}{\vec{g}}(x)v(x)\,dx+\int_{\Gamma}{\vec{g}}(x)\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox{\tiny$\;\bullet\;$}}{\vec{n}}(x)\,v(x)\,dx.

(U.20)
